POD Suboptimal Control of Evolution Problems Theory and Applications
S. Banholzer, D. Beermann, L. Mechelli, S. Volkwein

Abstract




Chapter 1
Introduction

The optimization and control of systems governed by
partial differential equations (PDEs)
usually requires numerous evaluations of the forward problem or the optimality system. Despite the fact that many recent efforts have been made to limit or reduce the number of evaluations to 5-10, this cannot be achieved in all situations and even if this is possible, these evaluations may still require a formidable computational effort. For situations, where this effort is not acceptable, model-order reduction can be a means to significantly reduce the required computational resources. In this work we utilize the method of proper orthogonal decomposition (POD) in order to numerically solve optimization problems governed by PDEs. This reduced-order approach is based on projecting the dynamical system onto subspaces consisting of basis elements that contain characteristics of the expected solution. This stands in contrast to, e.g., finite element techniques, where the basis elements of the subspaces do not relate to the physical properties of the system that they approximate.

1.1
Reduced-order modeling for optimal control problems

Optimal control problems for PDEs are often hard to tackle numerically because their discretization leads to very large-scale optimization problems. Therefore, different techniques of model reduction were developed to approximate these problems by smaller ones that are tractable with less effort.

The
balanced truncation method

[
Ant05
,
SvdVR08
,
ZDG96
]
is one well studied model reduction technique for state-space systems. This method utilizes the solutions to two Lyapunov equations, the so-called controllability and observability Gramians. The balanced truncation method is based on transforming the state-space system into a balanced form so that these Gramians become diagonal and equal. Moreover, the states that are difficult to reach or to observe, are truncated. The advantage of this method is that it preserves the asymptotic stability in the reduced-order system. Furthermore, a-priori error bounds are available. Recently, the theory of balanced truncation model reduction was extended to descriptor systems; see, e.g.,
[
MS05
]
and
[
HSS08
]
.

Recently the application of
reduced-order models
to linear, time varying and nonlinear systems, in particular to nonlinear control systems, has received an increasing amount of attention. The
reduced basis
(RB) method, as developed in
[
GMNP07
,
PR05
]
and
[
IR98
]
, is one such reduced-order method, where the basis elements correspond to the dynamics of expected control regimes. Let us refer to
[
DH13
,
HO14
,
NRMQ13
,
OS13
]
for the successful use of the reduced basis method in PDE constrained optimization problems. Currently,
POD
is probably the mostly used and most successful model reduction technique for nonlinear optimal control problems, where the basis functions contain information from the solutions of the dynamical system at pre-specified time-instances, so-called snapshots; see, e.g.,
[
Cha00
,
HLBR12
,
Sir87
,
Vol12
]
. Due to a possible linear or almost-linear dependence on the time variable, the snapshots themselves are not appropriate as a basis. Hence a singular value decomposition is carried out and the leading generalized eigenfunctions are chosen as a basis, referred to as the POD basis. POD is successfully used in a variety of fields including fluid dynamics, coherent structures
[
AH02
,
AFS00
]
and inverse problems
[
BJWW00
]
. Moreover, in
[
ABK01
]
POD is successfully applied to compute reduced-order controllers. The relationship between POD and balancing was considered in
[
LMG02
,
Row05
,
WP02
]
. An error analysis for nonlinear dynamical systems in finite dimensions was carried out in
[
RP02
]
and a missing point estimation in models described by POD was studied in
[
AWWB08
]
. Let us mention that POD and the reduced basis method are successfully combined by variants of the POD greedy algorithm; see
[
HO08
]
and
[
Haa12
]
, for instance.

Reduced-order models are used in PDE-constrained optimization in various ways; see, e.g.,
[
HV05
,
SV10
]
for a survey. In optimal control problems, it is sometimes necessary to compute a feedback control law instead of a fixed optimal control. In the implementation of these feedback laws, models of reduced order can play an important and very useful role, see
[
ABK01
,
KVX04
,
LV06
,
Rav00
]
. Another useful application is the use in optimization problems, where a PDE solver is part of the function evaluation. Obviously, thinking of a gradient evaluation or even a step-size rule in the optimization algorithm, an expensive function evaluation leads to an enormous amount of computing time. Here, the reduced-order model can replace the system given by a PDE in the objective function. It is quite common that a PDE can be replaced by a five- or ten-dimensional system of ordinary differential equations. This results computationally in a very fast method for optimization compared to the effort for the computation of a single solution of a PDE. There is a large amount of literature in engineering applications in this regard, we mention only the papers
[
LT01
,
NAM+03
]
. Recent applications can also be found in finance using the RB model
[
Pir09
]
and the POD model
[
SS05
,
Sch13
]
in the context of calibration for models in option pricing.

In the present work we use POD for deriving low order models of dynamical systems. These low order models then serve as surrogates for the dynamical system in the optimization process. We consider a linear-quadratic optimal control problem in an abstract setting and prove error estimates for the POD Galerkin approximations of the optimal control.

1.2
PDE constrained optimal control problems

To motivate the POD reduced-order modelling, we choose the following generic linear-quadratic optimal control problem: Suppose that the spatial
\Omega
is a bounded spatial
domain
in
\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{n}}
with
\mathfrak{n}\in\{1,2,3\}
. We write
{\bm{x}}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\mathfrak{n}})
for an element in
\Omega
. The (sufficiently smooth) boundary
\partial\Omega
is denoted by
\Gamma
. For given
T>0
we define the time-space cylinder
Q=(0,T)\times\Omega
and the boundary set
\Sigma=(0,T)\times\Gamma
. All controls are assumed to have the structure

\displaystyle u=(u^{\mathsf{d}},u^{\mathsf{b}}):\,(0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}}

with
{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}},\,{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}}\in\mathbb{N}
. The
set of admissible controls
has the simple form

\displaystyle{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}=\Big\{

\displaystyle u:(0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}}~\big|~u^{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathsf{a}i}(t)\leq u^{\mathsf{d}}_{i}(t)\leq u^{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathsf{b}i}(t),~i=1,...,{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}},

\displaystyle\hskip 120.92421ptu^{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{a}j}(t)\leq u^{\mathsf{b}}_{j}(t)\leq u^{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{b}j}(t),~j=1,...,{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}},t\in(0,T)\Big\},

where the functions
u^{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathsf{a}},u^{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathsf{b}}:(0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}
and
u^{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{a}},u^{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{b}}:(0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}}
are lower and upper control bounds satisfying
u^{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathsf{a}i}(t)\leq u^{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathsf{b}i}(t)
and
u^{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{a}j}(t)\leq u^{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{b}j}(t)
for all
i=1,...,{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}
,
j=1,...,{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}}
and
t\in(0,T)
.
For any admissible control
u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
, for a given (bounded) initial condition
y_{\circ}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
and for inhomogeneities
f:Q\to\mathbb{R}
,
g:\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}
, the state variable
y=y(t,{\bm{x}})
is the solution the the linear
heat equation
with convection which is a linear
parabolic partial differential equation
(PDE)

\displaystyle y_{t}(t,{\bm{x}})-\kappa\Delta y(t,{\bm{x}})+{\bm{v}}({\bm{x}})\cdot\nabla y(t,{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=f(t,{\bm{x}})+\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}u^{\mathsf{d}}_{i}(t)\chi_{i}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle\text{for }(t,{\bm{x}})\in Q,

(1.2.1a)

\displaystyle\kappa\frac{\partial y}{\partial{\bm{n}}}(t,{\bm{s}})+q({\bm{s}})y(t,{\bm{s}})

\displaystyle=g(t,{\bm{s}})+\sum_{j=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}}u^{\mathsf{b}}_{j}(t)\xi_{j}({\bm{s}})

\displaystyle\text{for }(t,{\bm{s}})\in\Sigma,

(1.2.1b)

\displaystyle y(0,{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=y_{\circ}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle\text{for }{\bm{x}}\in\Omega.

(1.2.1c)

In (
1.2.1
), the variables
\chi_{i}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
,
i=1,...,{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}
, and
\xi_{j}:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
,
j=1,...,{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}}
are
control shape functions
which indicate the effect of each control function in
Q
and
\Sigma
. Furthermore, the constant
\kappa>0
and the vector-valued function
{\bm{v}}=(v_{i})_{1\leq i\leq\mathfrak{n}}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{n}}
represent the diffusion and advection part of the equation while
q:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
with
q\geq 0
in
Q
is a boundary function. The dot between
{\bm{v}}({\bm{x}})
and the gradient of
y
in (
1.2.1a
) indicates the Euclidean inner product and is to be understood as

\displaystyle{\bm{v}}({\bm{x}})\cdot\nabla y(t,{\bm{x}})={\langle{\bm{v}}({\bm{x}}),\nabla y(t,{\bm{x}})\rangle}_{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{n}}v_{j}({\bm{x}})\frac{\partial y}{\partial{\bm{x}}_{j}}(t,{\bm{x}}),\quad(t,{\bm{x}})\in Q.

The term

\displaystyle\frac{\partial y}{\partial{\bm{n}}}(t,{\bm{s}})=\nabla y(t,{\bm{s}})\cdot{\bm{n}}({\bm{x}}),\quad(t,{\bm{s}})\in\Sigma,

stands for the (outward)
normal derivative
of
y
and
{\bm{n}}
denotes the outward normal vector. Let for any
u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
there exists a unique solution
y=y(u)
of (
1.2.1
). This issue will be discussed in detail in Section
3.2
. Let us introduce the generic
quadratic cost functional of tracking type
by

\displaystyle J(y,u)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{Q}}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\,\big|y(t,{\bm{x}})-y_{Q}(t,x)\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\alpha_{\Omega}}{2}\int_{\Omega}\,\big|y(T,{\bm{x}})-y_{\Omega}({\bm{x}})\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}\gamma^{\mathsf{d}}_{i}\,\big|u^{\mathsf{d}}_{i}(t)-u^{\mathsf{d}\mathsf{n}}_{i}(t)\big|^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}}\gamma^{\mathsf{b}}_{j}\,\big|u^{\mathsf{b}}_{j}(t)-u^{\mathsf{b}\mathsf{n}}_{j}(t)\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t,

where
\alpha_{Q},\alpha_{\Omega}\geq 0
are nonnegative
tracking weights
for the state and
\gamma^{\mathsf{d}}_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}})
,
\gamma^{\mathsf{b}}_{j}
(
1\leq j\leq{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}})
are positive
regularization parameters
for the controls. The functions
y_{Q}:Q\to\mathbb{R}
and
y_{\Omega}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
are given
desired states
and
{u^{\mathsf{n}}}=({u^{\mathsf{d}\mathsf{n}}},{u^{\mathsf{n}}}):(0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{m_{\mathsf{d}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{\mathsf{b}}}
is a chosen
nominal
or
expected control
. Now the optimal control problem is of the following form:

\min J(y,u)\quad\text{subject to (s.t.)}\quad u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}\text{ and }(y,u)\text{ satisfies \eqref{SIAM:EqMotPDE1}}.

(
\mathbf{OC}
)

Notice that (

OC

) is a
PDE-constrained quadratic programming problem
; cf.
[
BS12
,
HPUU09
,
IK08
,
Trö10
]
. Due to the fact that the state variable
y
depends on the control variable
u
via the state equations (
1.2.1
) we call (

OC

) an
optimal control problem
; cf.
[
Trö10
]
. Furthermore, (

OC

) is said to be a
linear-quadratic optimal control problem
, because the cost functional is quadratic and the state equation linear.

Since (
1.2.1
) possesses a unique solution
y=y(u)
for any
u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
, we are able to define the
reduced cost functional

\hat{J}
as

\displaystyle\hat{J}(u)=J(y(u),u)\quad\text{for }u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

Thus, (

OC

) can be equivalently written as the following
reduced optimal control problem

\min\hat{J}(u)\quad\text{subject to (s.t.)}\quad u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

(
\mathbf{\widehat{OC}}
)

If
\bar{u}
is a local solution to (

^
OC

), then
(\bar{y},\bar{u})
is a solution to (

OC

), where
\bar{y}=y(\bar{u})
denotes the unique solution to (
1.2.1
) for
u=\bar{u}
.

Remark 1.2.1
.

The term

\displaystyle-\kappa\Delta y(t,{\bm{x}})+{\bm{v}}({\bm{x}})\cdot\nabla y(t,{\bm{x}}),\quad(t,{\bm{x}})\in Q,

in (
1.2.1
) can be replaced by the generalized elliptic differential operator

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{A}y\big)(t,{\bm{x}})=-\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{n}}\bigg(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{n}}a_{ij}(t,{\bm{x}})\frac{\partial y}{\partial x_{j}}(t,{\bm{x}})\Big)+v_{i}(t,{\bm{x}})\frac{\partial y}{\partial x_{i}}(t,{\bm{x}})\bigg)+c(t,{\bm{x}})y(t,{\bm{x}})

for
(t,{\bm{x}})\in Q
with bounded coefficient functions
a_{ij}
,
v_{i}
and
c
. Moreover, more general objectives
J=J(y,u)
can be studied; see
[
HPUU09
,
Trö10
]
, for instance. To simplify the presentation we concentrate on the specific form (
1.2.1
).
\blacksquare

1.3
Guiding model problem for the numerical experiments

Let us introduce a particular instance of (

OC

) that describes a heat evolution phenomenon and will be used repeatedly for numerical illustrations throughout this book. We choose the two-dimensional unit square
\Omega=(0,1)^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
as the spatial domain and a final time of
T=5
. The (thermal) diffusivity is given as
\kappa=0.5
. The advection field
{\bm{v}}
comes from the solution of an incompressible Navier-Stokes equation and can be interpreted as the air velocity between an inflow at
\{0\}\times[0.75,1]
and an outflow at
\{1\}\times[0,0.25]
. The shape of the flow field is depicted alongside a sketch of the domain
\Omega
in Figure
1.3.1
.

1
0.75
0
1
0.25
0
0
0.2
0.4
0.6
0.8
1
\Gamma_{1}
\Gamma_{2}
\Gamma_{3}
\Gamma_{4}
\Omega_{1}
x_{2}
x_{1}

(a)

Geometry of the spatial domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

(b)

Velocity field
{\bm{v}}({\bm{x}})
.

Figure 1.3.1
:
Numerical setup in Example
1.3.1
.

We assume the existence of one distributed control
u^{\mathsf{d}}:(0,T)\to\mathbb{R}
acting on the subdomain
\Omega_{1}=([0.2,0.4]\cup[0.6,0.8])\times[0,0.1]
by a multiplication with a scaled indicator function
\chi:\Omega\to\{0,1\}
which takes the value
0.1
inside
\Omega_{1}
and
0
everywhere else. The inhomogeneity
f
is set to
0
. On the boundary, we interpret the function
q
as an insulation coefficient between the inside of
\Omega
and the ’outside world’ where we assume the temperature to be described by a function
y_{\mathsf{out}}(t)\in\mathbb{R}
for
t\in(0,T)
. In absence of a boundary control element, this insulation effect can be described by the linear model

\displaystyle\kappa\,\frac{\partial y}{\partial{\bm{n}}}(t,{\bm{s}})+q({\bm{s}})y(t,{\bm{s}})=q({\bm{s}})y_{\mathsf{out}}(t),\quad(t,{\bm{s}})\in\Sigma.

Notice that for every boundary point
{\bm{s}}\in\Gamma
and every time instance
t\in(0,T)
, the outward normal derivative
\tfrac{\partial y}{\partial{\bm{n}}}(t,{\bm{s}})
is proportional to the difference between the inside and outside temperature. In the notation of the general PDE (
1.2.1
), this means that the boundary inhomogeneity is given by
g(t,{\bm{s}})=q({\bm{s}})y_{\mathsf{out}}(t)
for all
(t,{\bm{s}})\in\Sigma
. For the insulation function, we define the boundary subsegments
\Gamma_{1}=\{0\}\times[0.75,1]
,
\Gamma_{2}=\{1\}\times[0,0.25]
,
\Gamma_{3}=[0,1]\times\{0\}
and
\Gamma_{4}=[0,1]\times\{1\}
, cf, Figure
1.1(a)
. The function
q
is then set to

\displaystyle q({\bm{s}})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &0.1&&\text{for }{\bm{s}}\in\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2},\\
&0&&\text{for }{\bm{s}}\in\Gamma_{3}\cup\Gamma_{4},\\
&0.01&&\text{otherwise}.\end{aligned}\right.

For the PDE model, this means that the upper and lower boundaries are assumed to be interior walls (with no interaction with the outside temperature) and that the insulation at the inflow and outflow segments
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
is comparably poorer than for the rest of these walls. Additionally, we add a single boundary control
u^{\mathsf{b}}:(0,T)\to\mathbb{R}
entering at the segment
\Gamma_{1}
with the scaled indicator function
\xi({\bm{s}})=0.1
if
{\bm{s}}\in\Gamma_{1}
and
\xi({\bm{s}})=0
everywhere else. Moreover, the initial temperature is assumed to be constant at
y_{\circ}({\bm{x}})=17
for all
{\bm{x}}\in\Omega
.

For the cost function
J
, we would like to track the desired temperature
y_{Q}(t,x)=17
for all
(t,{\bm{x}})\in Q
. Additionally, we add a final-time tracking of the same temperature at
y_{\Omega}({\bm{x}})=17
for all
{\bm{x}}\in\Omega
for the sake of numerical stability. Every deviation from the nominal controls
u^{\mathsf{d},\circ}(t)=0
and
u^{\mathsf{b},\circ}(t)=0
for
t\in(0,T)
are penalized with the regularization parameters
\gamma^{\mathsf{d}}=0.1
and
\gamma^{\mathsf{b}}=0.1
. In contrast, the state trackings are scaled by the parameters
\alpha_{Q}=1
and
\alpha_{\Omega}=0.1
.

Finally, we restrict the control variables to only take nonnegative values, which means that they can only be used to heat up, not cool down the system. Summarizing the linear-quadratic optimal control problem for our numerical experiments can now be described as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\min J(y,u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{5}\int_{\Omega}(y(t,{\bm{x}})-17)^{2}~\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\mathrm{d}t+\frac{0.1}{2}\int_{\Omega}(y(T,{\bm{x}})-17)^{2}~\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\\
&\hskip 62.59605pt+\frac{0.1}{2}\int_{0}^{5}u^{\mathsf{d}}(t)^{2}+u^{\mathsf{b}}(t)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\,\text{s.t. }0\leq u^{\mathsf{d}}(t),~0\leq u^{\mathsf{b}}(t)\text{ for }t\in(0,5)\text{ and}\\
&\hskip 11.38109pt\begin{aligned} &y_{t}(t,{\bm{x}})-0.5\Delta y(t,{\bm{x}})+{\bm{v}}({\bm{x}})\cdot\nabla y(t,{\bm{x}})=u^{\mathsf{d}}(t)\chi({\bm{x}}),&&(t,{\bm{x}})\in Q,\\
&\frac{\partial y}{\partial{\bm{n}}}(t,{\bm{s}})+q({\bm{s}})y(t,{\bm{s}})=q({\bm{s}})y_{\mathsf{out}}(t)+u^{\mathsf{b}}(t)\xi({\bm{s}}),&&(t,{\bm{s}})\in\Sigma,\\
&y(0,{\bm{x}})=y_{\circ}({\bm{x}}),&&{\bm{x}}\in\Omega.\end{aligned}\end{aligned}

(1.3.2)

For the outside temperature, we assume a periodic function given by
y_{\mathsf{out}}(t)=13+5\cos(2\pi t/5)
.

Run 1.3.1
.

Let us visualize the solution to the parabolic partial differential equation for three different control input functions, namely

\displaystyle u_{j}(t)=(u_{j}^{\mathsf{d}}(t),u_{j}^{\mathsf{b}}(t))^{\top}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &(0,0)^{\top},&j=1,\\
&(1.8,4.5)^{\top},&j=2,\\
&\big(1-\cos(2\pi t/T)\big)(1.8,4.5)^{\top},&j=3,\end{aligned}\right\}\quad t\in(0,T).

These lead to solutions
y_{j}(t,{\bm{x}})
for
(t,{\bm{x}})\in Q
, all
j=1,2,3
. The first control was chosen to see the solution without any control appearance. For the second control, we experimentally try to set both
u_{2}^{\mathsf{d}}(t)
and
u_{2}^{\mathsf{b}}(t)
to constant values which lead to a temperature evolution which is as close to
17
as we can get, for which we have settled at the nominated values. Lastly, we observe that the outer temperature negatively affects the inner temperature with its periodical intensity. We try to counteract this by choosing the likewise periodical control
u_{3}^{\mathsf{d}}
as denoted above. Figure
1.3.2
shows us the time-average solutions

\displaystyle\overline{y}_{j}(t)=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\int_{\Omega}y_{j}(t,{\bm{x}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}},\quad t\in(0,T)\text{ and }j=1,2,3,

where
|\Omega|
stands for the (Lebesgue) measure of the bounded set
\Omega
.

Figure 1.3.2
:
Run
1.3.1
. Time-average temperature evolution of the solutions to (
1.2.1
) for the fixed control inputs
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
, and
u_{3}
.

As we can see, not applying any control effort as in
u_{1}
results in a significant drop in temperature which follows the periodicity of the cosinal outside temperature
y_{\mathsf{out}}
. This effect is still visible for the constant control
u_{2}
which results in an overshoot of the target at
17
in the first half of the time horizon, and an undershoot in the second half. By setting the control
u_{3}
to be likewise periodical but inverse in orientation, this effect is counteracted and we get a very close approximation of the desired temperature. In Figure
1.3.3
, we can see so-called
snapshots
of the solutions at the time instances
t=2
and
t=4
.

Figure 1.3.3
:
Run
1.3.1
. Solution snapshots of the solutions to (
1.2.1
) for the fixed control inputs
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
, and
u_{3}
.

We can see that the advective cooldown effect from the outside temperature entering at
\Gamma_{1}
is most prevalent for the uncontrolled case. For the time-constant control
u_{2}
, it is also still visible, but we can also recognise the heating effect taking over at
t=4
in
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Omega_{1}
. For the periodical control
u_{3}
, it can be seen that the advection effect is almost entirely counteracted. The snapshot at
t=4
also identifies the regions close to the outside walls on the left and right as those which can not be heated as effectively with the current setup.
\Diamond

We will later see controls which appear as a result from optimization algorithms applied to the optimal control problem (
1.3.2
). It will interesting to see if they show a similar periodicity than our experimental candidate
u_{3}
.

1.4
Outline of the work

The work is organized as follows. In Chapter
2
we introduce the POD method in finite and infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces and discuss various applications. Chapter
3
is devoted to to POD-based Galerkin schemes for evolution problems. Mainly, we study linear problems taking different discretization methods into account. We provide a certified a-priori and a-posteriori error analysis. Furthermore, the numerical realizations are explained and illustrated by test examples. Quadratic programming problems governed by liner evolution problems are investigated in Chapter
4
. As in Chapter
3
we present a certified a-priori and a-posteriori error analysis. Moreover, we discuss basis update strategies. In Chapter
5
we give an outlook to further directions in reduced-order modeling in optimal control and optimization. More precisely, a nonlinear optimal control problem is studied. Moreover, state-constrained optimization problems are solved by a tailored combination of primal-dual active set methods and POD-aesed reduced-order modeling. Furthermore, POD Galerkin methods for multiobjective optimal control problems are investigated. Finally, some required theoretical foundations are summarized in the appendix.

Chapter 2
Proper Orthogonal Decomposition (POD)

In this chapter we introduce proper orthogonal decomposition (POD) which is a method for model-order reduction. The chapter is organized as follows:

\blacksquare

Section
2.1
introduces the discrete variant of the POD method. For a given, finite set of Hilbert space (data) vectors, we define and solve the problem of finding a low-dimensional POD subspace in which these vectors can be best approximated in a least-squares sense. We find that this subspace is spanned by the eigenvectors of a particular linear operator associated with the data vectors. The according eigenvalues are used to define an energy term which may be used as an indicator to find a suitable dimension of the POD subspace. We also consider some more concrete situations, which arises in numerical realizations, for the underlying problems such as unitary, Euclidean and finite-dimensional spaces as well as POD for finite-dimensional dynamical systems.

\blacksquare

In Section
2.2
we face the continuous variant of the POD method. In this case, there are infinitely many data vectors that depend on a variable (or parameter)
{\bm{\mu}}
from a predetermined set. Analogous to Section
2.1
we derive a formulation to approximate all data vectors in a least-squares optimal way by a low-dimensional linear subspace. Moreover, the basis vectors of this space take the form of another linear operator which bears a close resemblance to the operator from the discrete variant of the POD method.

\blacksquare

Section
2.3
concerns with the perturbation analysis for the POD basis. It deals with the case, where a continuous data trajectory as in Section
2.2
is discretized into a discrete set as in Section
2.1
. We compute the continuous and discrete POD bases to both trajectories and prove a stability result: If the discrete data trajectory approximates the continuous one in the limit, the discrete POD basis converges to the continuous one as well.

\blacksquare

In Section
2.4
, we discuss the specific choice when the underlying Hilbert space is part of a so-called Gelfand triple, a case which often occurs in evolution equations. It turns out that in this case it is possible to derive rate of convergence results which will be very useful in our a-priori error analysis carried out in Sections
3
and
4
.

\blacksquare

For better readability all proofs from this chapter have been moved to Section
2.5
.

Throughout this chapter we suppose that – unless stated otherwise –
X
is a separable complex Hilbert space endowed with the inner product
\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle_{X}
and induced norm
\|\cdot\|_{X}=\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle_{X}^{1/2}
.

2.1
The discrete variant of the POD method

Let us assume that we are given a snapshot space
\mathscr{V}^{n}
spanned by a finite number of vectors which belong to a separable complex Hilbert space. Then
\mathscr{V}^{n}
has a finite dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}
. The goal is to find an orthonormal set
V^{\ell}=\Span\{\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}\}\subset\mathscr{V}^{n}
with pairwise orthonormal vectors
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
such that the following properties hold:

\blacksquare

All elements in
\mathscr{V}^{n}
can be approximated sufficiently accurate by a linear combination of the vectors
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}
.

\blacksquare

The dimension
\ell
of
V^{\ell}
is as small as possible.

In this section we show that the POD method offers a way to compute the
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
in an optimal manner. It turns out that this approach is very much related to the eigendecomposition of a specific linear operator.

2.1.1
Problem formulation and main result

For fixed
n,K\in\mathbb{N}
let the so-called
snapshots

y_{1}^{k},\ldots,y_{n}^{k}\in X
be given for
1\leq k\leq{K}
. To avoid a trivial case we suppose that at least one of the
y_{j}^{k}
’s is non-zero. Then we introduce the finite dimensional, linear
snapshot space

\mathscr{V}^{n}=\mathrm{span}\,\Big\{y_{j}^{k}\,|\,1\leq j\leq n\text{ and }1\leq k\leq{K}\Big\}\subset X

(2.1.1)

with finite dimension
d^{n}\in\{1,\ldots,nK\}
. In Section
2.3
we will consider the case, where the number
n
tends to infinity. Therefore, we emphasize this dependence here by using the superscript
n
. We distinguish two cases:

1)

The separable Hilbert space
X
has finite dimension
m
: Then
X
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{m}
; see, e.g.,
[
RS80
, p. 47]
. We define the finite index set
\mathbb{I}=\{1,\ldots,m\}
. Clearly, we have
d^{n}\leq\min(n{K},m)
. Especially in case of
X=\mathbb{C}^{m}
, the snapshots
y_{j}^{k}=(y_{ij}^{k})_{1\leq i\leq m}
are vectors in
\mathbb{C}^{m}
.

2)

X
is infinite-dimensional: Since
X
is separable, each orthonormal basis of
X
has countably many elements. In this case
X
is isomorphic to the set
\ell_{2}
of sequences
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of complex numbers which satisfy
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}|x_{i}|^{2}<\infty
; see
[
RS80
, p. 47]
, for instance. The index set
\mathbb{I}
is now the countable, but infinite set
\mathbb{N}
.

Recall that a
complete
or
maximal basis

\{\psi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
in a separable Hilbert space
X
satisfies for any
\varphi\in X
:

\displaystyle{\langle\varphi,\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}=0\text{ for all }i\in\mathbb{I}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\varphi=0.

In this section we are facing the following problem.

Problem 2.1.2

(Discrete POD method)
.

The discrete method of POD consists in choosing a complete orthonormal basis
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
in
X
such that for every
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d^{n}\}
the mean square error between the
n{K}
elements
y_{j}^{k}
and their corresponding
\ell
-th partial Fourier sum is minimized on average:

\min\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\Big\|y_{j}^{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{X}^{2}\text{ s.t. }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset X\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{X}=\delta_{ij},~1\leq i,j\leq\ell,

(
\mathbf{P}^{\ell}_{n}
)

where
\omega_{k}^{K}
and
\alpha_{j}^{n}
are positive weighting parameters for
k=1,\dots,{K}
and
j=1,\dots,n
. Here, the symbol
\delta_{ij}
denotes the Kronecker symbol satisfying
\delta_{ii}=1
and
\delta_{ij}=0
for
i\neq j
.

Definition 2.1.3
.

An optimal solution
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
to Problem
2.1.2
is called a POD basis of rank
\ell
.

Remark 2.1.4
.

To ease the presentation the weights
\alpha_{j}^{n}
are chosen independently of
k\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
. Moreover,
n
could depend on
k\in\{1,\ldots,{K}\}
as well, with a generalization of the presented theory and algorithms being straightforward.
\blacksquare

Main result:
In Theorem
2.1.14
we prove that for every
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d^{n}\}
a solution
\{\hat{\psi}^{n}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
to (

P
ℓ

n

) is characterized by the eigenvalue problem

\mathcal{R}^{n}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\quad\text{for }1\leq i\leq\ell,

(2.1.2)

where
\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{n}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{d^{n}}^{n}>0
denote the largest positive eigenvalues of the linear, bounded, non-negative and self-adjoint operator

\mathcal{R}^{n}:X\to X,\quad\mathcal{R}^{n}\psi=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\langle\psi,y_{j}^{k}\rangle}_{X}y_{j}^{k}\quad\text{for }\psi\in X.

(2.1.3)

Furthermore, we obtain the following approximation
error formula

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\Big\|y_{j}^{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\Big\|_{X}^{2}=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}.

Remark 2.1.5
.

The decay of the eigenvalues
\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{d^{n}}
plays an essential role for a successful application of the POD method. In general, one has to utilize a complete orthonormal basis in
X
to represent elements in the snapshot space
\mathscr{V}^{n}
by their Fourier sum. This leads to a high-dimensional or even infinite-dimensional approximation scheme. Nevertheless, if the finite sum
\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}
is sufficiently small for a not too large
\ell
, elements in the subspace
\mathscr{V}^{n}
can be approximated by a linear combination of the few basis elements
\{\hat{\psi}^{n}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
. This offers the chance to reduce the number of terms in the Fourier series utilizing the POD basis of rank
\ell
, as shown in the following examples.
\blacksquare

2.1.2
Three motivating examples

Before we focus on the mathematical theory we illustrate the POD method for three different examples. In the first one we show how to compress the information of a given function, which is depending on a time and a spatial variable, by using POD and apply this to the case of three functions being composed of sine and cosine functions.

Example 2.1.6

(Three simple functions)
.

We introduce a regular function
y:Q\to\mathbb{R}
, where
Q=(0,T)\times\Omega
and
\Omega=({\bm{x}}_{\mathsf{a}},{\bm{x}}_{\mathsf{b}})\subset\mathbb{R}
are given with
T>0
and
{\bm{x}}_{\mathsf{a}}<{\bm{x}}_{\mathsf{b}}
. These sets are equidistantly discretized into
n
and
n_{\bm{x}}
points, respectively, as
0=t_{1}<\ldots<t_{n}=T
and
{\bm{x}}_{\mathsf{a}}={\bm{x}}_{1}<\ldots<{\bm{x}}_{n_{\bm{x}}}={\bm{x}}_{\mathsf{b}}
with constant step sizes

\displaystyle\Delta t=\frac{T}{n-1}>0,\quad h=\frac{{\bm{x}}_{\mathsf{b}}-{\bm{x}}_{\mathsf{a}}}{n_{\bm{x}}-1}>0.

The function
y
is approximated pointwise by introducting the
n
vectors
y_{j}=(\mathrm{y}_{ij})_{1\leq i\leq n_{\bm{x}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\bm{x}}}
with
\mathrm{y}_{ij}=y(t_{j},{\bm{x}}_{i})
for
i=1,...,n_{\bm{x}}
and
j=1,...,n
. In
X=\mathbb{R}^{n_{\bm{x}}}
we introduce a weighted inner product to represent a trapezoidal integration scheme over the
{\bm{x}}
-variable :

\displaystyle{\langle\mathrm{u},\mathrm{v}\rangle}_{X}=h\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{u}_{1}\mathrm{v}_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{n_{\bm{x}}-1}\mathrm{u}_{i}\mathrm{v}_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{u}_{n_{\bm{x}}}\mathrm{v}_{n_{\bm{x}}}\bigg)=\mathrm{u}^{\top}{\bm{W}}\mathrm{v}\quad\text{for }\mathrm{u},\mathrm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\bm{x}}}

with the weighting matrix
{\bm{W}}=h\,\mathrm{diag}\,(1/2,1,\ldots,1,1/2)\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\bm{x}}\times n_{\bm{x}}}
. The associated norm is given as
\|\cdot\|_{X}=\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle_{X}^{1/2}
. Similarly, we define the trapezoidal weights for the time variable as

\alpha_{1}^{n}=\frac{\Delta t}{2},\quad\alpha_{j}^{n}=\Delta t\text{ for }j=2,...,n-1,\quad\alpha_{n}^{n}=\frac{\Delta t}{2}

(2.1.4)

and consider the following instance of (

P
ℓ

n

):

\min\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\Big\|y_{j}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{X}^{2}\text{ s.t. }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset X\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{X}=\delta_{ij},~1\leq i,j\leq\ell.

(2.1.5)

Problem (
2.1.5
) translates to finding
X
-orthonormal vectors
\psi_{1},...,\psi_{\ell}\in X
that approximate the discretized trajectory
\{y_{1},...,y_{n}\}\subset X
of the function
y
as best as possible in a least-square-sense. We call
\{y_{1}^{\ell},\ldots,y_{n}^{\ell}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n_{\bm{x}}}
given by
y_{j}^{\ell}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\langle y_{j},\psi_{i}\rangle_{X}\psi_{i}
the
\ell
-th POD approximation of
y
. In our numerical example, we consider the three functions

\displaystyle y_{1}:(0,2\pi)\times(0,2\pi)\to\mathbb{R},\quad y_{1}(t,{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\cos(t)\cos({\bm{x}}),

\displaystyle y_{2}:(0,2\pi)\times(0,2\pi)\to\mathbb{R},\quad y_{2}(t,{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\cos(t+{\bm{x}}),

\displaystyle y_{3}:(0,1)\times(0,2\pi)\to\mathbb{R},\quad y_{3}(t,{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\cos(t{\bm{x}}).

Each of these functions is discretized as described above and various POD approximations are computed by solving (
2.1.5
) with methods that will be explained in detail in Section
2.1.7
. We denote the solutions by
\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}
for
i=1,...,\ell
.

Figure 2.1.1
:
Example
2.1.6
. Function
y_{1}(t,{\bm{x}})=\cos(t)\cos({\bm{x}})
(left plot); projection of
y_{1}
onto the POD space
V^{\ell}=\Span\{\hat{\psi}_{1}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\psi}_{\ell}^{n}\}
for
\ell=1
(right plot).

In Figure
2.1.1
we observe that only one basis function
\hat{\psi}_{1}^{n}
is enough to approximate the trajectory of
y_{1}
. This is due to the fact that the spatial and temporal variable are separated, meaning that the trajectory of
y_{1}
is in fact one-dimensional, so each snapshot can be represented by a simple scaling of the
\cos({\bm{x}})
function.

Figure 2.1.2
:
Example
2.1.6
. Function
y_{2}(t,{\bm{x}})=\cos(t+{\bm{x}})
(left plot); projection of
y_{2}
onto the POD space
V^{\ell}=\Span\{\hat{\psi}_{1}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\psi}_{\ell}^{n}\}
for
\ell=1
(middle plot) and
\ell=2
(right plot).

For
y_{2}
, two basis functions
\hat{\psi}_{1}^{n}
,
\hat{\psi}_{2}^{n}\in X
are required to represent the trajectory; see Figure
2.1.2
. The reason for this lies in the addition theorem for trigonometrical functions:

\displaystyle\cos(t+{\bm{x}})=\cos(t)\cos({\bm{x}})-\sin(t)\sin({\bm{x}})

The two POD bases functions
\hat{\psi}_{1}^{n}
and
\hat{\psi}_{2}^{n}
together span the same subspace as
\cos
and
\sin
, the trajectory can be fully represented by its POD approximations for
\ell=2
.

Figure 2.1.3
:
Example
2.1.6
. Function
y_{3}(t,{\bm{x}})=\cos(t{\bm{x}})
(left plot); projection of
y_{3}
onto the POD space
V^{\ell}=\Span\{\hat{\psi}_{1}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\psi}_{\ell}^{n}\}
for
\ell=2
(middle plot) and
\ell=5
(right plot).

In Figure
2.1.3
we observe that the qualitative behaviour of
y_{3}
is represented quite well by its POD approximation for
\ell=5
, while its approximation for
\ell=2
lacks the sharp features of the original trajectory. There is no analytical property that allows us to decompose the term
\cos(t{\bm{x}})
as it was done for
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
. Hence, more basis functions are required to sufficiently capture the behaviour of
y_{3}
, as can also be seen in Figure
2.1.4
.

Figure 2.1.4
:
Example
2.1.6
. Decay of the POD approximation error
\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}
for the three functions
y_{1}
,
y_{2}
and
y_{3}
.

We refer to
[
HLBR12
, Section 3.3.3]
, where sufficient conditions are given so that the POD bases are Fourier modes.
\blacklozenge

For the second example, we return to the parabolic evolution problem from Section
1.2
and present a typical situation how the discrete POD problem (

P
ℓ

n

) might arise in such a setting.

Example 2.1.7

(Evolution problem)
.

Let us consider the parabolic problem introduced in Section
1.2
for
{K}
different initial conditions
\{y_{\circ}^{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
In particular, we choose functions which have a doubled Gaussian temperature distribution added to the otherwise constant initial condition of
y_{\circ}({\bm{x}})=17
for all
{\bm{x}}\in\Omega=(0,1)^{2}
:

\displaystyle y_{\circ}^{i,j}({\bm{x}})=17+\frac{2}{\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma_{j}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{|{\bm{x}}-p^{(i)}|_{2}^{2}}{2\sigma_{j}^{2}}\bigg).

Here,
|\cdot|_{2}
denotes the Euclidian norm,
p^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
are the distribution peaks and
\sigma_{i}>0
are different standard deviations. The latter are chosen as

\displaystyle p^{(i)}_{1}=0.15,~p^{(i)}_{2}\in\big\{0.1,0.3,0.5,0.7.0.9\big\},\quad\sigma_{j}\in\big\{0.05,0.1,0.5,1\big\}.

All possible combinations of
p^{(i)}
and
\sigma_{j}
give us
K=20
different initial conditions. Since the initial peak temperature is always located on the left-hand side and the advective flow depicted in Figure
1.1(b)
is oriented from the top left to the lower right, this peak will be transported to different levels of effectiveness by the advection effect. For the discretization of the time variable, we choose a temporal grid
0=t_{1}<t_{2}<\ldots<t_{n}=T
with the step sizes
\delta t_{j}=t_{j}-t_{j-1}
for
j=2,\ldots,n
. Let

\displaystyle\delta t=\min_{2\leq j\leq n}\delta t_{j}\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta t=\max_{2\leq j\leq n}\delta t_{j}

be the minimal and maximal time step size, respectively. By
y_{j}^{k}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
,
1\leq j\leq n
and
1\leq k\leq{K}
, we denote the solution to (
1.2.1
) at the time instance
t_{j}
for initial condition
y^{k}_{\circ}
and fixed control
u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
. For
\alpha_{j}^{n}
we take trapezoidal weights:

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}^{n}=\frac{\delta t_{1}}{2},\quad\alpha_{j}^{n}=\frac{\delta t_{j}+\delta t_{j-1}}{2}\text{ for }j=2,\ldots,n-1,\quad\alpha_{n}^{n}=\frac{\delta t_{n}}{2}.

Then we can interpret the term

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\Big\|y_{j}^{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{X}^{2}

as a trapezoidal approximation of the integral term

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\Big\|y^{k}(t,\cdot)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{k}(t,\cdot),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{X}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t.

The choice of the quadrature weights should be related to the (discretization) error of the approximations
y_{j}^{k}
. For example, the trapezoidal rule leads to an error of the size
\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})
, but if the discretization error is of higher order, then other quadrature rules, like e.g. the Simpson rule, should be utilized. A usual choice for the Hilbert space
X
is the Lebesgue space
H=L^{2}(\Omega)=L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
; cf., Definition
A.4.55
. However, we can also choose
X=V
with the Sobolev space
V=H^{1}(\Omega)=H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
; cf., Definition
A.4.57
. Since we do not want to weight the solutions for distinct initial conditions differently, we take
\omega_{k}^{K}=1
. Figure
2.1.5
shows the decay of the first
25
eigenvalues
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}
for the cases
X=V
and
X=H
.

Figure 2.1.5
:
Example
2.1.7
. Eigenvalue decay with the choices
X=V=H^{1}(\Omega)
and
X=H=L^{2}(\Omega)
.

Concentrating at this time only on the
V
-line, we can see that by the tenth eigenvalue, the order of magnitude has decreased from roughly
10^{4}
to
10^{1}
. At the
20
-th eigenvalue, we have arrived at
10^{-1}
and consideration of further eigenvectors is not necessary to capture the essential information contained in all
K=20
snapshots trajectories. In Figure
2.1.6
, we can observe the first six orthonormal eigenvectors
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{6}\in V=H^{1}(\Omega)
for
X=V
.

Figure 2.1.6
:
Example
2.1.7
. First six POD basis vectors
\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}
with
X=V=H^{1}(\Omega)
.

We can see that the first three functions mainly capture the advection effect which is oriented from the upper left corner
\Gamma_{1}
to the lower right corner
\Gamma_{2}
. After this, we can start to observe some circles on the left-hand sides of
\psi_{5}
and
\psi_{6}
which correspond to the intensity peaks from the initial conditions located between
p^{(1)}=(0.1,0.1)^{\top}
and
p^{(5)}=(0.1,0.9)^{\top}
. The fact the “advective” POD vectors come before those belonging to the initial condition together with the fast decay in eigenvalues after
\ell=3
tells us that the main challenge for the POD basis is not the varying initial conditions in this case, but the advection part. Note that this effect is only strengthened by the low outside temperature which may enter very effectively at the upper-left, poorly insulated inflow segment
\Gamma_{1}
. Finally, let us mention that one could also discuss different control inputs
u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
instead of, or in addition to, the various initial conditions.
\blacklozenge

The third motivating example for the discrete POD method takes the form of a generic elliptic equation arising in the field of acoustics.

Example 2.1.8

(Elliptic problem)
.

The absorption behavior of trim parts inside a car is described by the frequency dependent impedance
Z(f)\in\mathbb{C}
. The acoustical simulations of car interior noise is governed by the well-known Helmholtz equation. A very important parameter in this model is the frequency-dependent impedance
Z(f)\in\mathbb{C}
(see left plot of Figure
2.1.7
), which the frequency-dependent absorption behavior of trim parts inside a car. Let a simplified interior of a car be given by the two-dimensional cross section
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
plotted in the right plot of Figure
2.1.7
; cf.
[
LV14
,
Vol10
,
VH08
]
.

Figure 2.1.7
:
Example
2.1.8
. Real and imaginary part of the impedance
Z(f)
for the damping material Melamin
50 mm
over the frequency interval from
200
to
500 Hz
(left plot); spatial domain
\Omega
where
\Gamma_{\mathrm{R}}
is shown by the dashed line ant the bottom (right plot); cf
[
LV14
,
Vol10
,
VH08
]
.

The boundary
\Gamma=\partial\Omega
is split into the disjoint parts
\Gamma_{\mathrm{R}}=\{(x_{1},0)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,|\,0.5\leq x_{1}\leq 1,75\}
(impedance part) and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{N}}=\Gamma\setminus\Gamma_{\mathrm{R}}
(rigid body part). Furthermore, let the constants

c=343.799\,\frac{\mathrm{m}}{\mathrm{s}},\quad k(f)=\frac{2\pi f}{c},\quad\varrho_{\circ}=1.19985\,\frac{\mathrm{kg}}{\mathrm{m}^{3}},\quad\omega(f)=2\pi f=ck(f)

(2.1.6)

be given, where
k(f)
and
\omega(f)
are called the frequency-dependent
wave number
and
angular frequency
, respectively. In the right plot of Figure
2.1.7
we see the complex-valued
impedance
for the fire-resistant damping material Melamin
50\,\mathrm{mm}
in the frequency range from
200
to
500\,\mathrm{Hz}
. The complex-valued
sound pressure

p(f)=p(\cdot\,;f):\Omega\to\mathbb{C}
is governed by the
Helmholtz equation
, which is the elliptic boundary value problem

\displaystyle-\Delta p(f)-k(f)^{2}p(f)

\displaystyle=q(f)

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega,

(2.1.7)

\displaystyle\frac{\jmath}{\varrho_{\circ}\omega(f)}\,\frac{\partial p(f)}{\partial{\bm{n}}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{on }\Gamma_{\mathrm{N}},

\displaystyle\frac{\jmath}{\varrho_{\circ}\omega(f)}\,\frac{\partial p(f)}{\partial{\bm{n}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{p(f)}{Z(f)}

\displaystyle\text{on }\Gamma_{\mathrm{R}}

for every frequency
f\in\mathscr{F}=[200,500]
. In (
2.1.7
) the function
q(f)
stands for the
f
-dependent source term modelling the excitation at the point
{\bm{x}}_{q}=(0.28,1.21)\in\Omega
with the frequency
f
:

\displaystyle q({\bm{x}};f)=\frac{1}{5}\exp\left(\frac{\jmath\pi(f-200)}{50}\right)\exp\left(-50\,{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}_{q}|}_{2}^{2}\right)\quad\text{for }{\bm{x}}\in\Omega,~f\in\mathscr{F}.

The existence of a weak solution to (
2.1.7
) follows from the Fredholm alternative; see, e.g.,
[
Eva08
, pp. 640-644]
. For more details we refer to
[
Vol10
, Theorem 2.2]
.

Next we apply the discrete POD method with
{K}=1
and
\omega_{1}^{K}=1
. For
n=301
, we choose the equidistant frequency grid
f_{j}=199+j
for
j=1,\ldots,n
. We utilize the (trapezoidal) weights

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}^{n}=\frac{1}{2},\quad\alpha_{j}^{n}=1\text{ for }j=2,\ldots,n-1,\quad\alpha_{n}^{n}=\frac{1}{2}.

Let
p(f_{j}):\Omega\to\mathbb{C}
,
j=1,\ldots,n
, be the (approximate) weak solutions to (
2.1.7
) for the frequency
f_{j}\in\mathscr{F}
. In Example
2.1.25
we will utilize piecewise linear finite elements to compute numerical solutions. Similarly to Example
2.1.7
a natural choice for the Hilbert space
X
is the Lebesgue space
H=L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C})
. Of course, we could also choose
X=V
with
V=H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C})
. The discrete POD method (

P
ℓ

n

) then takes the form

\left\{\begin{aligned} \min\quad&\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\Big\|p(f_{j})-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle p(f_{j}),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{H}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{H}^{2}\\
\text{s.t.}\quad&\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset H\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{H}=\delta_{ij},~1\leq i,j\leq\ell.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.1.8)

It corresponds to the determination of the subspace in which the solution of (
2.1.7
) to each discrete frequency
f_{j}
can approximated in the closest possible way.

In Figure
2.1.8
, the decay of the normalized eigenvalues
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}/\sum_{j=1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{n}\in[0,1]
is presented for the two choices
X=H
(left plot) and
X=V
(right plot).

Figure 2.1.8
:
Example
2.1.8
. Decay of the normalized eigenvalues
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}/\sum_{j=1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{n}
are presented for the two choices
X=H
(left plot) and
X=V
(right plot).

We observe that the decay rates for the real and imaginary parts of the normalized eigenvalues behave similarly. Of course, the decay rate is slower for the stronger
V
-topology than for the weaker
H
-topology; compare Example
2.1.7
and Figure
2.1.5
. We also refer to
[
HDPV06
]
for more details.
\blacklozenge

Remark 2.1.9
.

In Examples
2.1.7
and
2.1.8
we have introduced two separable Hilbert spaces
H
and
V
, which satisfy
V\subset H
. Furthermore,
V
is continuously and densely embedded into
H
. This offers special properties for the POD method. We will address this issue in Section
2.4
.
\blacklozenge

2.1.3
Derivation of the main result

Recall the eigenvalue problem (
2.1.2
) and the linear operator
\mathcal{R}^{n}
introduced in (
2.1.3
). In the next lemma we summarize properties of the operator
\mathcal{R}^{n}
ensuring the existence of non-negative eigenvalues
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{d^{n}}^{n}>0
. A proof is given in Section
2.5.1
.

Lemma 2.1.10
.

Let
y_{1}^{k},\ldots,y_{n}^{k}\in X
be given snapshots for
1\leq k\leq{K}
. Define the linear operator
\mathcal{R}^{n}:X\to X
as follows:

\mathcal{R}^{n}\psi=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\langle\psi,y_{j}^{k}\rangle}_{X}\,y_{j}^{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\overline{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi\rangle}_{X}\,y_{j}^{k}\quad\text{for }\psi\in X

(2.1.9)

with positive weights
\omega_{1}^{K},\ldots,\omega_{K}^{K}
and
\alpha_{1}^{n},\ldots,\alpha_{n}^{n}
. Then
\mathcal{R}^{n}
is a compact, self-adjoint and non-negative operator.

Remark 2.1.11
.

Since
X
is a separable complex Hilbert space and
\mathcal{R}^{n}:X\to X
is a linear, compact, self-adjoint and non-negative operator, we can utilize the Riesz-Schauder and Hilbert-Schmidt theorems (Theorems
A.3.49
and
A.3.50
, respectively) as well as Remark
A.2.47
: It follows that there exist a complete countable orthonormal basis
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
and a corresponding sequence of real eigenvalues
\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
satisfying

\mathcal{R}^{n}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n},\quad\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{n}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{d^{n}}^{n}>\hat{\lambda}_{d^{n}+1}^{n}=\ldots=0.

(2.1.10)

The spectrum of
\mathcal{R}^{n}
is a pure point spectrum except for possibly
0
. Each non-zero eigenvalue of
\mathcal{R}^{n}
has finite multiplicity and
0
is the only possible accumulation point of the spectrum of
\mathcal{R}^{n}
.
\blacksquare

The next result is proved in Section
2.5.1
, too.

Lemma 2.1.12
.

Let
y_{1}^{k},\ldots,y_{n}^{k}\in X
be given snapshots for
1\leq k\leq{K}
and the linear operator
\mathcal{R}^{n}:X\to X
be defined as in (
2.1.9
).

1)

We have

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\big|{\langle y_{j}^{k},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}={\langle\mathcal{R}^{n}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\quad\text{for any }i\in\mathbb{I}.

(2.1.11)

In particular, it follows that

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\big|{\langle y_{j}^{k},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}=0\quad\text{for all }i>d^{n}.

(2.1.12)

2)

The identity

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|y_{j}^{k}\|}_{X}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}.

(2.1.13)

holds.

3)

The set
\{\hat{\psi}_{1}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\psi}_{d^{n}}^{n}\}
forms an orthonormal basis of
\mathscr{V}^{n}
.

4)

The objective of (

P
ℓ

n

) can be written as

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\Big\|y_{j}^{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{X}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\big|{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}.

(2.1.14)

Remark 2.1.13
.

Due to Lemma
2.1.12
-2) and -4) the minimization problem (

P
ℓ

n

) is equivalent to

\min-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\big|{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}\text{ s.t. }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset X\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{X}=\delta_{ij},~1\leq i,j\leq\ell.

(
\mathbf{\hat{P}}^{\ell}_{n}
)

\blacksquare

Now we formulate the main result for (

P
ℓ

n

) and (

^
P

ℓ

n

). For the proof we again refer the reader to Section
2.5.1
.

Theorem 2.1.14
.

Let
y_{1}^{k},\ldots,y_{n}^{k}\in X
for
1\leq k\leq{K}
be given snapshots and
\mathcal{R}^{n}:X\to X
be defined by (
2.1.9
). Suppose that
\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
and
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
denote the non-negative eigenvalues and associated orthonormal eigenfunctions of
\mathcal{R}^{n}
satisfying (
2.1.10
). Then for every
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d^{n}\}
, the first
\ell
eigenfunctions
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
solve (

P
ℓ

n

) and (

^
P

ℓ

n

). Moreover, the values of the costs evaluated at the optimal solution
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
satisfy

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\Big\|y_{j}^{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\Big\|_{X}^{2}=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}

(2.1.15)

and

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\big|{\langle y_{j}^{k},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}.

(2.1.16)

Example 2.1.15
.

Let us return to Example
2.1.6
, where the trajectories of three time- and space-dependent functions
y_{i}
(
i=1,2,3
) are captured using POD bases. We infer from (
2.1.15
) and (
2.1.16
) that the decay rate of the eigenvalues
\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{n},\hat{\lambda}_{2}^{n},\ldots
can be regarded as an indicator of the quality of a POD approximation.

Figure 2.1.9
:
Example
2.1.15
. Decay of the eigenvalues
\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\lambda}_{10}^{n}
for the three basic functions
y_{1}
,
y_{2}
and
y_{3}
from Example
2.1.6
.

In Figure
2.1.9
this decay is plotted over the index
i
. We can see that
\hat{\lambda}_{2}^{n},\hat{\lambda}_{3}^{n},\ldots
are essentially zero for the first function
y_{1}(t,x)=\cos(t)\cos(x)
. This fits our theoretical and numerical observation from Example
2.1.6
that this trajectory is one-dimensional. The same is true for
y_{2}(t,x)=\cos(t+x)
where
\hat{\lambda}_{3}^{n},\hat{\lambda}_{4}^{n},\ldots
vanish in accordance with the two-dimensionality of the trajectory. Lastly, the eigenvalues of
y_{3}(t,x)=\cos(tx)
decay much slower and more uniformly, indicating to us that there is no inherent low-dimensional subspace that will be able to fully capture this trajectory.
\blacklozenge

2.1.4
Relationship to singular value decomposition

POD is strongly related to the
singular value decomposition
(SVD) for linear operators. To show this relationship, let us endow the linear space
\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}
of complex-valued
({K}\times n)
matrices with the weighted inner product

\displaystyle{\langle{\bm{\Phi}},{\bm{\Psi}}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\Phi_{kj}\overline{\Psi}_{kj}\quad\text{for }{\bm{\Phi}}=\big(\big(\Phi_{kj}\big)\big),\,{\bm{\Psi}}=\big(\big(\Psi_{kj}\big)\big)\in\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n},

where the weights
\omega_{1}^{K},\ldots,\omega_{K}^{K}
and
\alpha_{1}^{n},\ldots,\alpha_{n}^{n}
are assumed to be positive. Furthermore, we utilize the induced norm

\displaystyle{\|{\bm{\Phi}}\|}_{\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}}={\langle{\bm{\Phi}},{\bm{\Phi}}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}}^{1/2}=\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,|\Phi_{kj}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\text{ for }{\bm{\Phi}}=\big(\big(\Phi_{kj}\big)\big)\in\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}.

Recall that for fixed
n,{K}\in\mathbb{N}
we have introduced the snapshots
y_{1}^{k},\ldots,y_{n}^{k}\in X
for
1\leq k\leq{K}
at the beginning of Section
2.1.1
. Now we define the linear operator
\mathcal{Y}^{n}:\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}\to X
as

\mathcal{Y}^{n}{\bm{\Phi}}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\Phi_{kj}y_{j}^{k}\quad\text{for }{\bm{\Phi}}=\big(\big(\Phi_{kj}\big)\big)\in\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}.

(2.1.17)

Properties of
\mathcal{Y}^{n}
are presented in the next lemma which is proved in Section
2.5.1
.

Lemma 2.1.16
.

1)

The operator
\mathcal{Y}^{n}
is bounded and compact.

2)

Its adjoint operator
\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}=(\mathcal{Y}^{n})^{\star}:X\to\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}
of
\mathcal{Y}^{n}
is given by

\displaystyle(\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}\psi)_{kj}=\langle\psi,y^{k}_{j}\rangle_{X}\quad\text{for }1\leq k\leq{K}\text{ and }1\leq j\leq n

for all
\psi\in X
.

3)

The operator
\mathcal{Y}^{n}\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}:X\to X
is linear, bounded, compact, self-adjoint and non-negative. In particular, it holds
\mathcal{Y}^{n}\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}=\mathcal{R}^{n}
, where the operator
\mathcal{R}^{n}
has been introduced in (
2.1.9
).

Remark 2.1.17
.

In particular, by introducing the operator
\mathcal{Y}^{n}
we have proved Lemma
2.1.10
in a different way.
\blacksquare

Suppose that
\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
and
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
denote the non-negative eigenvalues and associated orthonormal eigenfunctions of
\mathcal{R}^{n}
given by Theorem
2.1.14
. Then we define

\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}=\sqrt{\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}}>0\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{\bm{\Phi}}^{n}_{i}=\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}}\,\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\in\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,d^{n}.

(2.1.18)

The SVD of the linear operator
\mathcal{Y}^{n}
is considered in the next lemma, which is proved in Section
2.5.1
.

Lemma 2.1.18
.

The elements
\{\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{d^{n}}
,
\{\hat{\bm{\Phi}}^{n}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d^{n}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{K\times n}
and
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{d^{n}}\subset X
form the SVD of the operator
\mathcal{Y}^{n}
, i.e.
\{\hat{\bm{\Phi}}^{n}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d^{n}}
and
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{d^{n}}
are respectively orthonormal in
\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}
and
X
, and it holds

\mathcal{Y}^{n}\hat{\bm{\Phi}}^{n}_{i}=\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\in X\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}=\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}\hat{\bm{\Phi}}_{i}^{n}\in\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,d^{n}.

(2.1.19)

Moreover, we have the representation

y_{j}^{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{d^{n}}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{d^{n}}\overline{\big(\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\big)}_{kj}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}\overline{\big(\hat{\Phi}_{i}^{n}\big)}_{kj}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}

(2.1.20)

for
1\leq k\leq{K}
and
1\leq j\leq n
.

Remark 2.1.19
.

Let the liner operator
\mathcal{K}^{n}
be given by
\mathcal{K}^{n}=\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}\mathcal{Y}^{n}:\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}\to\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}
. Then, for any
{\bm{\Phi}}=((\Phi_{\nu l}))\in\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}
the components of the matrix
\mathcal{K}^{n}{\bm{\Phi}}\in\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}
are given as

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{K}^{n}{\bm{\Phi}}\big)_{kj}=\Bigg(\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=1}^{K}\omega_{\nu}^{K}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\alpha_{l}^{n}\Phi_{\nu l}y_{l}^{\nu}\bigg)\Bigg)_{kj}=\sum_{\nu=1}^{K}\omega_{\nu}^{K}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\alpha_{l}^{n}\Phi_{\nu l}{\langle y^{\nu}_{l},y_{j}^{k}\rangle}_{X}

for
1\leq k\leq{K}
and
1\leq j\leq n
. It follows from Lemma
2.1.16
that
\mathcal{K}^{n}
is compact, self-adjoint and non-negative. In particular, (
2.1.19
) implies that

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{n}\hat{\bm{\Phi}}_{i}^{n}=\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}\mathcal{Y}^{n}\hat{\bm{\Phi}}_{i}^{n}=\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\hat{\bm{\Phi}}_{i}^{n}\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,d^{n}.

Consequently, the matrices
\{\hat{\bm{\Phi}}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{d^{n}}
are orthonormal eigenvectors of
\mathcal{K}^{n}
associated with the positive eigenvalues
\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{n}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{d^{n}}^{n}>0
.
\blacksquare

Next we use the SVD of the operator
\mathcal{Y}^{n}
to develop an elementwise error estimate for the POD in the sense of (
2.1.15
). For the proof we refer the reader to Section
2.5.1
.

Lemma 2.1.20
.

Let
(k,j)\in\{1,\ldots,{K}\}\times\{1,\ldots,n\}
be arbitrary. Then it holds

\omega_{k}^{K}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\Big\|y_{j}^{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\Big\|_{X}^{2}\leq\hat{\lambda}_{\ell+1}^{n}.

(2.1.21)

2.1.5
Computational aspects for POD

For the application of POD to concrete problems the choice of
\ell
is certainly of central importance. It appears that no general a-priori rules are available. Rather the choice of
\ell
is based on heuristic considerations combined with observing the ratio of the
relative total energy
contained in the snapshots
y_{1}^{k},\ldots,y_{n}^{k}
,
1\leq k\leq{K}
, which is expressed by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\ell)=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}}{\sum_{i=1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}}\in[0,1].

Utilizing (
2.1.10
) we have

\mathcal{E}(\ell)=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|y_{j}^{k}\|}_{X}^{2}},

(2.1.22)

i.e., the computation of the eigenvalues
\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}\}_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}
is not necessary. This is utilized in numerical implementations when iterative eigenvalue solvers like the Lanczos method; see
[
Ant05
, Chapter 10]
, are applied. In these kind of solvers not all eigenvalues are computed, but only the first
\ell
largest one. Let us summarize the computation of a POD basis of rank
\ell
in Algorithm
2.1.5
.

Remark 2.1.21
.

We will address the solution of the eigenvalue problems in lines 2 and 8 in the Sections
2.1.7
-
2.1.10
.
\blacksquare

0:

Snapshots
\{y_{j}^{k}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset X
for
1\leq k\leq{K}
and rank
\ell\leq d^{n}
or tolerance
0<\varepsilon_{\mathsf{POD}}\ll 1
;

1:

Compute
\Lambda^{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\|y_{j}^{k}\|_{X}^{2}
;

2:

if

\ell
is given
then

3:

Compute pairs
\{(\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n})\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
by solving (
2.1.10
) with
\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{n}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\lambda}^{n}_{\ell}
and
\langle\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n},\hat{\psi}_{j}^{n}\rangle_{X}=\delta_{ij}
for
i,j=1,\ldots,\ell
;

4:

Set
\mathcal{E}(\ell)=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}/\Lambda^{n}
;

5:

else

if

\varepsilon_{\mathsf{POD}}
is given
then

6:

Set
i=0
and
\mathcal{E}(0)=0
;

7:

while

\mathcal{E}(i)\leq 1-\varepsilon_{\mathsf{POD}}

and

i<d^{n}

do

8:

Set
i=i+1
;

9:

Compute a pair
(\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n})
by solving
\mathcal{R}^{n}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}
subject to
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\geq\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{n}
for
j=i+1,\ldots,d^{n}
and
\langle\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n},\hat{\psi}_{j}^{n}\rangle_{X}=\delta_{ij}
for
j=1,\ldots,i-1
;

10:

Define
\mathcal{E}(i)=\mathcal{E}(i-1)+\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}/\Lambda^{n}
;

11:

end

while

12:

Set
i=\ell
;

13:

end

if

14:

return
POD basis
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
, eigenvalues
\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
and ratio
\mathcal{E}(\ell)
;

2.1.6
POD-greedy method for multiple snapshots

Let us mention the following approach for multiple snapshots which is related to the
POD-greedy algorithm
for parametrized evolution problems; cf.
[
Gre12
,
Haa12
,
HO08
]
, for instance. Suppose again that we are given
{K}
different snapshot trajectories
\{y_{j}^{k}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset X
for
k=1,\ldots,{K}
. Starting with
\nu=1
, we choose a
k_{\nu}=k_{1}\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
and consider the
snapshot space
defined by the
k_{\nu}
-th trajectory

\mathscr{V}^{n}_{\nu}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{y_{\nu j}\,\big|\,1\leq j\leq n\big\}\subset X,\quad y_{\nu j}=y_{j}^{k_{\nu}}\text{ for }j=1,\ldots,n

(2.1.23)

with the finite dimension
d^{n}_{\nu}=\dim\mathscr{V}^{n}_{\nu}\leq n
. We define the linear operator

\mathcal{R}^{n}_{\nu}\psi=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\langle y_{\nu j},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,y_{\nu j}\quad\text{for }\psi\in X,

(2.1.24)

which is compact, self-adjoint and non-negative by Lemma
2.1.10
. Suppose that
\{\hat{\lambda}_{\nu i}^{n}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
and
\{\hat{\psi}_{\nu i}^{n}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
are the non-negative eigenvalues and associated orthonormal eigenfunctions satisfying

\mathcal{R}^{n}_{\nu}\hat{\psi}_{\nu i}^{n}=\hat{\lambda}_{\nu i}^{n}\hat{\psi}_{\nu i}^{n},\quad\hat{\lambda}_{\nu 1}^{n}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{\nu d_{\nu}^{n}}^{n}>\hat{\lambda}_{\nu d_{\nu}^{n}+1}^{n}=\ldots=0.

(2.1.25)

For a given
\ell_{\nu}\leq d^{n}_{\nu}
, the eigenfunctions
\{\hat{\psi}_{\nu i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell_{\nu}}
solve

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\Big\|y_{\nu j}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell_{\nu}}{\langle y_{\nu j},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{X}^{2}\text{ s.t. }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell_{\nu}}\subset X\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{X}=\delta_{ij},~1\leq i,j\leq\ell_{\nu},

i.e.,
\{\hat{\psi}^{n}_{\nu i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell_{\nu}}
is a POD basis of rank
\ell_{\nu}
for the snapshot space
\mathscr{V}^{n}_{\nu}
. In the next step we test if this POD basis leads also to a good POD basis for the other snapshot ensembles
\{y_{j}^{k}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset X
for
k\neq k_{1}
. If this is not the case, we add a POD basis of the snapshot ensemble
\{y_{j}^{k_{2}}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset X
,
k_{2}\in\{1,\ldots,k\}\setminus\{k_{1}\}
, who leads to the worst approximation quality to the already computed POD bases functions. The method in summarized in Algorithm
2.1.6
.

0:

Snapshots
\{y_{j}^{k}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset X
for
1\leq k\leq{K}
and rank
\ell\leq d^{n}
or tolerance
0<\varepsilon_{\mathsf{POD}}\ll 1
;

1:

Set
\nu=1
,
\ell=0
and
\mathscr{N}=\emptyset
;

2:

repeat

3:

Compute the projection errors

\displaystyle\mathsf{err}_{\nu}^{\ell}(k)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\Big\|y_{j}^{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\Big\|_{X}^{2}\quad\text{for }k\in\big\{1,\ldots,{K}\big\}\setminus\mathscr{N};

4:

Determine the index
k_{\nu}\in\argmax\{\mathsf{err}_{\nu}^{\ell}(k)\,|\,k\in\{1,\ldots,{K}\}\setminus\mathscr{N}\}
;

5:

Set
\mathscr{N}=\mathscr{N}\cup\{k_{\nu}\}
;

6:

Choose the snapshots
y_{\nu j}=y_{j}^{k_{\nu}}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{k_{\nu}},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}
for
j=1,\ldots,n
;

7:

Solve (
2.1.25
) to get a POD basis
\{\hat{\psi}^{n}_{\nu i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell_{\nu}}
satisfying
\mathsf{err}_{\nu}^{\ell}(k_{\nu})\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{POD}}
;

8:

Set
\hat{\psi}_{\ell+i}^{n}=\hat{\psi}^{n}_{\nu i}
for
i=1,\ldots,\ell_{\nu}
and put
\ell=\ell+\ell_{\nu}
;

9:

until

\max\big\{\mathsf{err}_{\nu}^{\ell}(k)\,|\,k=1,\ldots,{K}\big\}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{POD}}

or

\nu={K}

10:

return
POD basis
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
and eigenvalues
\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
;

Remark 2.1.22
.

1)

In steps 3 and 6 we set

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{k_{\nu}},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}=0,\quad k_{\nu}\in\big\{1,\ldots,{K}\big\}

for
\ell=0
. In particular, we have

\displaystyle k_{1}\in\argmax\bigg\{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\big\|y_{j}^{k}\big\|_{X}^{2}\,\Big|\,k\in\{1,\ldots,{K}\}\bigg\}

for
\ell=0
.

2)

By construction we get an orthonormal set
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
. However, the
Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization

[
TB22
, pp. 56-61]
should be employed to ensure this property numerically.
\blacksquare

2.1.7
POD in the unitary spaces

Let us discuss the POD method for the specific case
X=\mathbb{C}^{m}
with
m\in\mathbb{N}
and a single snapshot ensemble, meaning that we set
{K}=1
and
\omega_{1}^{K}=1
. Then we have
n
snapshot vectors
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
and introduce the rectangular matrix
{\bm{Y}}=[y_{1}\,|\ldots|\,y_{n}]\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
with rank
d^{n}\leq\min(m,n)
. Choosing
\alpha_{j}^{n}=1
for
1\leq j\leq n
, Problem (

P
ℓ

n

) has the form

\min\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big\|y_{j}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}^{2}\text{ s.t. }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset\mathbb{C}^{m}\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}=\delta_{ij},~1\leq i,j\leq\ell.

(2.1.26)

In (
2.1.17
) we have introduced the operator
\mathcal{Y}^{n}
, which is now of the simplified form

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}^{n}\phi=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\phi_{j}y_{j}={\bm{Y}}\phi\quad\text{for }\phi\in\mathbb{C}^{n},

i.e.,
\mathcal{Y}^{n}={\bm{Y}}
holds true. This implies
\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}={\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}
, where “
\mathsf{H}
” stands for the Hermitian on
{\bm{Y}}
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{R}^{n}={\bm{Y}}{\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}
holds true. We infer that (
2.1.10
) leads to the symmetric
m\times m
eigenvalue problem

{\bm{Y}}{\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n},\quad\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{n}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{d^{n}}^{n}>\hat{\lambda}_{d^{n}+1}^{n}=\ldots=\hat{\lambda}_{m}^{n}=0.

(2.1.27)

We have discussed the
singular value decomposition
(SVD) for linear operators in Section
2.1.4
. Since
\mathcal{Y}^{n}
is just the matrix
{\bm{Y}}
, the SVD for linear operators coincides with the well-known SVD for matrices
[
TB22
, pp. 25-30]
: There exist real numbers
\hat{\sigma}_{1}^{n}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\sigma}_{d^{n}}^{n}>0
and unitary matrices
{\bm{\Psi}}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}
with column vectors
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{m}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
{\bm{\Phi}}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
with column vectors
\{\hat{\phi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

{\bm{\Psi}}^{\mathsf{H}}{\bm{Y}}{\bm{\Phi}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{\bm{D}}&0\\
0&0\end{array}\right)=:{\bm{\Sigma}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n},

(2.1.28)

where
{\bm{D}}=\mathrm{diag}\,(\hat{\sigma}_{1}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\sigma}_{d^{n}}^{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{n}\times d^{n}}
and the zeros in (
2.1.28
) denote matrices of appropriate dimensions. Moreover the vectors
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{d^{n}}
and
\{\hat{\phi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{d^{n}}
satisfy

{\bm{Y}}\hat{\phi}_{i}^{n}=\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\quad\text{and}\quad{\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}=\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}\hat{\phi}_{i}^{n}\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,d^{n}.

(2.1.29)

They are eigenvectors of
{\bm{Y}}{\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}
and
{\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}{\bm{Y}}
, respectively, with eigenvalues
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}=(\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n})^{2}>0
,
i=1,\ldots,d^{n}
. The vectors
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=d^{n}+1}^{m}
and
\{\hat{\phi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=d^{n}+1}^{n}
(if
d^{n}<m
respectively
d^{n}<n
) are eigenvectors of
{\bm{Y}}{\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}
and
{\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}{\bm{Y}}
with eigenvalue
0
. Consequently, in the case
n<m
one can determine the POD basis of rank
\ell
as follows: Compute the eigenvectors
\hat{\phi}_{1}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\phi}_{\ell}^{n}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
by solving the symmetric
n\times n
eigenvalue problem

{\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}{\bm{Y}}\hat{\phi}_{i}^{n}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\hat{\phi}_{i}^{n}\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,\ell

(2.1.30a)

and set, by (
2.1.29
),

\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}=\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}}\,{\bm{Y}}\hat{\phi}_{i}^{n}\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,\ell.

(2.1.30b)

Recall that by Theorem
2.1.14
we have

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big\|y_{j}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\,\hat{\psi}_{i}\Big\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}^{2}=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}(\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n})^{2}.

(2.1.31)

From (
2.1.21
) we deduce that

\displaystyle\Big\|y_{j}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\Big\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\leq\hat{\sigma}_{\ell+1}^{n}\quad\text{for every }j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}.

Thus, the
(\ell+1)
-th singular value gives an upper bound for the approximation error for each snapshot vector
y_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,n
.

Remark 2.1.23
.

1)

For historical reasons the method of determining the POD basis by solving (
2.1.30
) is sometimes called the
method of snapshots
; see
[
Sir87
]
. For
i\in\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
let us denote by
(\hat{\phi}_{i})_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq n
the
j
-th component of the eigenvector
\hat{\phi}_{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Using
{\bm{Y}}=[y_{1}\,|\ldots|\,y_{n}]
and (
2.1.30b
) we find that the
i
-th POD basis is given by the snapshots
\{y_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
as follows:

\displaystyle\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}=\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}}\,{\bm{Y}}\hat{\phi}_{i}^{n}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}}(\hat{\phi}_{i})_{j}\bigg)y_{j}\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,\ell.

2)

If
m<n
holds, we compute the POD basis by solving the
m\times m
eigenvalue problem (
2.1.27
). If the matrix
{\bm{Y}}
is badly scaled, we should avoid to build the matrix product
{\bm{Y}}{\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}
(or
{\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}{\bm{Y}}
). In this case the SVD turns out to be more stable for the numerical computation of the POD basis of rank
\ell
.
\blacksquare

Note that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}{\|y_{j}\|}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}^{2}={\|{\bm{Y}}\|}_{F}^{2}\quad\text{for }j=1,\ldots,n

holds, where the Frobenius norm
\|\cdot\|_{F}
has been recalled in Section
A.1
. Thus, we infer from (
2.1.22
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\ell)=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}{\|y_{j}\|}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}^{2}}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}}{{\|{\bm{Y}}\|}_{F}^{2}}.

Let
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{m}\subset\mathbb{C}^{m}
be a solution to (
2.1.27
) with associated eigenvalues
\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{m}
. Since
\{\hat{\psi}_{1}^{n},...,\hat{\psi}_{d^{n}}^{n}\}
form an orthonormal basis of
\mathscr{V}^{n}
, we have

y_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{d^{n}}{\langle y_{j},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\quad\text{for any }j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}.

(2.1.32)

We define the matrices
\hat{\bm{\Psi}}^{d^{n}}=[\hat{\psi}_{1}^{n}\,|\ldots,|\hat{\psi}_{d^{n}}^{n}]\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times d^{n}}
and
\hat{\bm{B}}^{d^{n}}
by
\hat{B}^{d^{n}}_{ij}=\langle y_{j},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}
for
1\leq i\leq d^{n}
,
1\leq j\leq n
. Then we can write (
2.1.32
) as
{\bm{Y}}=\hat{\bm{\Psi}}^{d^{n}}\hat{\bm{B}}^{d^{n}}
. From (
2.1.31
) it follows that

\displaystyle{\|{\bm{Y}}-\hat{\bm{\Psi}}^{\ell}\hat{\bm{B}}^{\ell}\|}_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big|Y_{ij}-\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\hat{\Psi}_{ik}^{\ell}\hat{B}_{kj}\Big|^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\Big|Y_{ij}-\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\hat{\Psi}_{ik}^{\ell}\Big|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big\|y_{j}-\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\hat{\psi}_{k}\Big\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}^{2}=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}(\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n})^{2},

where
\hat{\bm{\Psi}}^{\ell}
contains the first
\ell
columns of
\hat{\bm{\Psi}}^{d^{n}}
and
\hat{\bm{B}}^{\ell}
the first
\ell
rows of
\hat{\bm{B}}^{d^{n}}
.

The next results follows from (
2.1.26
) and (
2.1.27
). It states that for every
\ell\leq d^{n}\leq m
the approximation of the columns of
{\bm{Y}}
by the first
\ell
singular vectors
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
is optimal with respect to the Frobenius norm among all rank
\ell
approximations to the columns of
{\bm{Y}}
. For a proof we refer to Section
2.5.1
.

Corollary 2.1.24
.

Let all hypotheses of Theorem
2.1.14
hold.

1)

Optimality of the POD method
: Suppose that
{\bm{\Psi}}^{d^{n}}=[\psi_{1}\,|\ldots|\,\psi_{d^{n}}]\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times d^{n}}
denotes a matrix with
d^{n}
pairwise orthonormal vectors
\psi_{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
and that the expansion of the columns of
{\bm{Y}}
in the basis
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d^{n}}
is given by

\displaystyle{\bm{Y}}={\bm{\Psi}}^{d^{n}}{\bm{B}}^{d^{n}},\quad\text{where}\quad B^{d^{n}}_{ij}={\langle y_{j},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\quad\text{for }1\leq i\leq d^{n},1\leq j\leq n.

Then for every
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d^{n}\}
we have

{\|{\bm{Y}}-\hat{\bm{\Psi}}^{\ell}\hat{\bm{B}}^{\ell}\|}_{F}\leq{\|{\bm{Y}}-{\bm{\Psi}}^{\ell}{\bm{B}}^{\ell}\|}_{F}.

(2.1.33)

In (
2.1.33
) the matrix
{\bm{\Psi}}^{\ell}
contains the first
\ell
columns of
{\bm{\Psi}}^{d^{n}}
and
{\bm{B}}^{\ell}
are the first
\ell
rows of
{\bm{B}}^{d^{n}}
. Similarly,
\hat{\bm{\Psi}}^{\ell}
and
\hat{\bm{B}}^{\ell}
are defined.

2)

Uncorrelated POD coefficients: We have

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\overline{\langle y_{j},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}{\langle y_{j},\hat{\psi}_{k}^{n}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\delta_{ik}\quad\text{for }1\leq i,k\leq\ell.

In Algorithm
2.1.7
we summarize the steps for the computation of a POD basis of rank
\ell
for the specific case
X=\mathbb{C}^{m}
.

0:

Snapshots
\{y_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset\mathbb{C}^{m}
, POD rank
\ell\leq d^{n}
and
flag
for the solver;

1:

Set
{\bm{Y}}=[y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}]\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
;

2:

if

flag
= 0
then

3:

Compute singular value decomposition
[{\bm{\Psi}},{\bm{\Sigma}},{\bm{\Phi}}]=\mathrm{svd}\,({\bm{Y}})
;

4:

Set
\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}=\Psi_{\cdot,i}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
and
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}={\bm{\Sigma}}_{ii}^{2}
for
i=1,\ldots,\ell
;

5:

else

if

flag
= 1
then

6:

Determine
\mathcal{R}^{n}={\bm{Y}}{\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}
;

7:

Compute eigenvalue decomposition
[{\bm{\Psi}},{\bm{\Lambda}}]=\mathrm{eig}\,(\mathcal{R}^{n})
;

8:

Set
\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}={\bm{\Psi}}_{\cdot,i}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
and
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}=\Lambda_{ii}
for
i=1,\ldots,\ell
;

9:

else

if

flag
= 2
then

10:

Determine
\mathcal{K}^{n}={\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}{\bm{Y}}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
;

11:

Compute eigenvalue decomposition
[{\bm{\Phi}},{\bm{\Lambda}}]=\mathrm{eig}\,(\mathcal{K}^{n})
;

12:

Set
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}=\Lambda_{ii}
and
\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}={\bm{Y}}{\bm{\Phi}}_{\cdot,i}/\Lambda_{ii}^{1/2}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
for
i=1,\ldots,\ell
;

13:

end

if

14:

Compute
\mathcal{E}(\ell)=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}/{\|{\bm{Y}}\|}_{F}^{2}
;

15:

return
POD basis
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
, eigenvalues
\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
and ratio
\mathcal{E}(\ell)
;

2.1.8
POD in Euclidean spaces

Let us briefly discuss the special case
X=\mathbb{R}^{m}
, where we keep all other choices of Section
2.1.7
. We proceed in the same way, with the exception to consider the transpose “
\top
” instead of the hermitian conjugate “
\mathsf{H}
”. More precisely, (
2.1.27
) reads

{\bm{Y}}{\bm{Y}}^{\top}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n},\quad\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{n}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{d^{n}}^{n}>\hat{\lambda}_{d^{n}+1}^{n}=\ldots=\hat{\lambda}_{m}^{n}=0

(2.1.34)

for the snapshot matrix
{\bm{Y}}=((Y_{ij}))\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
with
{\bm{Y}}=[y_{1}\,|\ldots|\,y_{m}]
. Let us explain for the special case
X=\mathbb{R}^{m}
how we can derive (
2.1.34
) by techniques of non-linear programming. To simplify the presentation here, we consider only the case
\ell=1
and suppose that
\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{n}>\hat{\lambda}_{2}^{n}
holds. In that case, (

^
P

ℓ

n

) is equivalent to the maximization problem

\max\sum_{j=1}^{n}{\langle y_{j},\psi\rangle}_{2}^{2}\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\psi=\big(\uppsi_{i}\big)_{1\leq i\leq m}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\text{ and }{|\psi|}_{2}=1.

(
\mathbf{\hat{P}}^{1}_{n}
)

Problem (

^
P

1

n

) is a constrained optimization problem. Therefore, we define the
Lagrange functional

\mathcal{L}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
associated with (

^
P

1

n

) by
i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\psi,\mu)=-\sum_{j=1}^{n}{\langle y_{j},\psi\rangle}_{2}^{2}+\mu e(\psi)\quad\text{for }(\psi,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R},

where
e:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
is given by
e(\psi)=|\psi|^{2}_{2}-1
for
\psi\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Suppose that
\hat{\psi}^{n}=(\hat{\uppsi}_{i}^{n})_{1\leq i\leq m}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a solution to (

^
P

1

n

). Then
\nabla e(\hat{\psi}^{n})=2(\hat{\psi}^{n})^{\top}\neq 0
holds. Thus,
\hat{\psi}^{n}
is.a
regular point
for the constrained function
e(\cdot)
and
first-order necessary optimality conditions

[
NW06
, Chapter 12]
for (

^
P

1

n

) are given as follows: there exists an associated (unique) Lagrange multiplier
\hat{\mu}^{n}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\displaystyle\nabla\mathcal{L}(\hat{\psi}^{n},\hat{\mu}^{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}0\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}.

We compute the gradient of
\mathcal{L}
with respect to
\psi
. For any component
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
we get

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\psi_{i}}\,(\hat{\psi}^{n},\hat{\mu}^{n})=\frac{\partial}{\partial\psi_{i}}\,\Bigg(-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bigg|\sum_{k=1}^{m}Y_{kj}\hat{\uppsi}_{k}^{n}\bigg|^{2}+\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{m}(\hat{\uppsi}_{k}^{n})^{2}-1\bigg)\hat{\mu}^{n}\Bigg)=2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bigg(-\sum_{k=1}^{m}Y_{kj}\hat{\uppsi}_{k}^{n}\bigg)Y_{ij}+2\hat{\mu}^{n}\hat{\uppsi}_{i}^{n}

\displaystyle\quad=2\sum_{k=1}^{m}\bigg(-\sum_{j=1}^{n}Y_{ij}({\bm{Y}})^{\top}_{jk}\hat{\uppsi}_{k}^{n}\bigg)+2\hat{\mu}^{n}\hat{\uppsi}_{i}^{n}=2\sum_{k=1}^{m}\big(-{\bm{Y}}{\bm{Y}}^{\top})_{ik}\hat{\uppsi}_{k}^{n}\big)+2\hat{\mu}^{n}\hat{\uppsi}_{i}^{n}.

Thus,

\nabla_{\psi}\mathcal{L}(\hat{\psi}^{n},\hat{\mu}^{n})=-2\big({\bm{Y}}{\bm{Y}}^{\top}\hat{\psi}^{n}-\hat{\mu}^{n}\hat{\psi}^{n}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}0\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{m}.

(2.1.35)

For
\hat{\mu}^{n}=\hat{\lambda}^{n}
