equation (
2.1.35
) yields the eigenvalue problem (
2.1.34
). From

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\lambda}(\hat{\psi}^{n},\hat{\lambda}^{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}0\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}

we infer the constraint
|\hat{\psi}^{n}|_{2}=1
. However, it is not clear yet which eigenvalue-eigenvector pair
(\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n})
is a solution to (

^
P

1

n

). Therefore, let us turn to
second-order optimality conditions

[
NW06
, Chapter 12]
. Note that
\nabla_{\psi\psi}\mathcal{L}(\psi,\mu)=-2({\bm{Y}}{\bm{Y}}^{\top}-\mu{\bm{I}})\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
holds for any
(\psi,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}
. Let
\psi\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
be chosen arbitrarily. Let
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{m}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
denote
m
orthonormal eigenvectors of
{\bm{Y}}{\bm{Y}}^{\top}
satisfying (
2.1.34
). Then we can write
\psi
in the form

\displaystyle\psi=\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\langle\psi,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{2}\,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}.

At
(\hat{\psi}^{n},\hat{\lambda}^{n})=(\hat{\psi}^{n}_{1},\hat{\lambda}^{n}_{1})
we conclude from
\hat{\lambda}^{n}_{1}\geq\hat{\lambda}_{2}^{n}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{m}^{n}\geq 0
that

\displaystyle{\langle\psi,\nabla_{\psi\psi}\mathcal{L}(\hat{\psi}^{n},\hat{\lambda}^{n})\psi\rangle}_{2}=-2\,{\langle\psi,\big({\bm{Y}}{\bm{Y}}^{\top}-\hat{\lambda}^{n}{\bm{I}}\big)\psi\rangle}_{2}

\displaystyle=-2\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}{\langle\psi,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{2}{\langle\psi,\hat{\psi}_{j}^{n}\rangle}_{2}{\langle\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n},\big({\bm{Y}}{\bm{Y}}^{\top}-\hat{\lambda}^{n}{\bm{I}}\big)\hat{\psi}_{j}^{n}\rangle}_{2}

\displaystyle=-2\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\big(\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{n}-\hat{\lambda}^{n}\big)\,{\langle\psi,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{2}{\langle\psi,\hat{\psi}_{j}^{n}\rangle}_{2}{\langle\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n},\hat{\psi}_{j}^{n}\rangle}_{2}

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i=1}^{m}\big(\hat{\lambda}^{n}-\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\big){\langle\psi,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{2}^{2}\geq 0.

(2.1.36)

Thus,
(\hat{\psi}^{n},\hat{\lambda}^{n})
satisfies the
second-order necessary optimality conditions
for a minimum. Additionally, we get that every eigenvalue-eigenvector pair
(\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n})
with
\lambda_{i}^{n}<\lambda_{1}^{n}
does not fulfill the necessary second-order condition.

The
second-order sufficient optimality conditions
would require
\nabla_{\psi\psi}\mathcal{L}(\hat{\psi}^{n},\hat{\lambda}^{n})
to be positive definite on the tangential hyperplane

\displaystyle\ker\big(\nabla e(\hat{\psi}^{n})^{\top}\big)=\big\{\psi\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\,\big|\,{\langle\hat{\psi}^{n},\psi\rangle}_{2}=0\big\}=\{\hat{\psi}^{n}\}^{\perp}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\hat{\psi}_{2}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\psi}_{m}^{n}\big\}.

Inserting
\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}
into (
2.1.36
) for an arbitrary
i\in\{2,\ldots,m\}
, we get the condition
\hat{\lambda}^{n}>\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}
. Therefore, the sufficient second-order conditions hold true if
\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{n}>\hat{\lambda}_{2}^{n}
. Otherwise,
\psi=\hat{\psi}_{2}^{n}
is a non-zero vector from the tangential hyperplane that satisfies
\langle\hat{\psi}_{2}^{n},\nabla_{\psi\psi}\mathcal{L}(\hat{\psi}^{n},\hat{\lambda}^{n})\hat{\psi}_{2}^{n}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}=0
, so that
\hat{\psi}^{n}
is only a saddle point. In this case,
\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{n}=\hat{\lambda}_{2}^{n}
implies that
\hat{\psi}_{2}^{n}
is also a global solution to (

^
P

1

n

), as can be seen from (
2.1.11
). To prove that
\hat{\psi}^{n}
actually solves (

^
P

1

n

) one has to proceed as in the proof of Theorem
2.1.14
. The observation that a multiplicity of solutions to (

^
P

1

n

) coincides with second-order sufficient optimality conditions not holding true carries over to the case
\ell>1
: If
\hat{\lambda}_{\ell}^{n}>\hat{\lambda}_{\ell+1}^{n}
, then it can be shown that second-order sufficient optimality conditions hold for
\hat{\psi}_{1}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\psi}_{\ell}^{n}
.

2.1.9
POD with weighted inner product

Let us return to the unitary case of Section
2.1.7
, but this time with the inclusion of the weights
\alpha_{1}^{n},\ldots,\alpha_{n}^{n}
from the original problem (

P
ℓ

n

) and the inner product in
X=\mathbb{C}^{m}
being given by a positive definite and Hermitian
weighting matrix

{\bm{W}}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}
:

{\langle\psi,\phi\rangle}_{\bm{W}}=\psi^{\top}\overline{{\bm{W}}\phi}={\langle\psi,{\bm{W}}\phi\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}}^{m}\quad\text{for }\psi,\phi\in\mathbb{C}^{m}

(2.1.37)

The associated norm is defined as
|\cdot|_{\bm{W}}=\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle_{\bm{W}}^{1/2}
. From
{\bm{W}}={\bm{W}}^{\mathsf{H}}
we infer that

\displaystyle{\langle\psi,\psi\rangle}_{\bm{W}}=\psi^{\top}\overline{{\bm{W}}\psi}=\big(\psi^{\top}\overline{{\bm{W}}\psi}\big)^{\top}=\overline{\psi}^{\top}{\bm{W}}^{\mathsf{H}}\psi=\overline{\psi}^{\top}{\bm{W}}\psi=\overline{\psi^{\top}\overline{{\bm{W}}\psi}}=\overline{{\langle\psi,\psi\rangle}}_{\bm{W}}

for every
\psi\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
. Consequently,
\langle\psi,\psi\rangle_{\bm{W}}\in\mathbb{R}
holds for every
\psi\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
.

We choose
{K}=1
and
\omega_{1}^{K}=1
. Then problem (

P
ℓ

n

) has the form

\displaystyle\min\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\Big|y_{j}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}\,\psi_{i}\Big|_{\bm{W}}^{2}\text{ s.t. }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset\mathbb{C}^{m}\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}=\delta_{ij},~1\leq i,j\leq\ell,

where the associated induced norm is given as usual by
|\cdot|_{\bm{W}}=\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle_{\bm{W}}^{1/2}
. Let us now motivate the inner product by the three examples from Section
2.1.2
. In all cases, we are dealing with discretized representations of infinite-dimensional functions, for which the inner product matrix
{\bm{W}}
is supposed to represent the finite-dimensional version of the underlying function space’s inner product.

Example 2.1.25
.

1)

In Example
2.1.5
, each data vector
y_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{{\bm{x}}}}
was representative of the function
y(t_{j},\cdot):({\bm{x}}_{\mathsf{a}},{\bm{x}}_{\mathsf{b}})=\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
. A natural inner product space for these types of functions is given by the space
H=L^{2}(\Omega)
of square integrable functions; cf. Definition
A.4.55
:

\displaystyle{\langle f,g\rangle}_{H}=\int_{{\bm{x}}_{\mathsf{a}}}^{{\bm{x}}_{\mathsf{b}}}f({\bm{x}})g({\bm{x}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\quad\text{for }f,g\in H.

We want to approximate the inner product in
H
by a quadrature rule. This can be done by introducing an appropriate weighting matrix
{\bm{W}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{{\bm{x}}}\times n_{{\bm{x}}}}
and a weighted inner product in
\mathbb{R}^{n_{\bm{x}}}
. A natural way to do this has been already introduced in Example
2.1.5
itself by use of trapezoidal integration: it holds for all
f,g\in H

\displaystyle{\langle f,g\rangle}_{H}\approx h\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{f}_{1}^{h}\mathrm{g}_{1}}{2}+\sum_{i=2}^{n_{{\bm{x}}}-1}\mathrm{f}_{i}^{h}\mathrm{g}_{i}^{h}+\frac{\mathrm{f}_{n_{\bm{x}}}^{h}\mathrm{g}_{n_{\bm{x}}}^{h}}{2}\bigg)=(\mathrm{f}^{h})^{\top}{\bm{W}}\mathrm{g}^{h},

where the vector
\mathrm{f}^{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{{\bm{x}}}}
is the discretization of
f
given by the
n_{\bm{x}}
components

\displaystyle\mathrm{f}^{h}_{i}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{2}{h}\int_{{\bm{x}}_{1}}^{{\bm{x}}_{1}+h/2}f({\bm{x}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}&&\text{for }i=1,\\
&\frac{1}{h}\int_{{\bm{x}}_{i}-h/2}^{{\bm{x}}_{i}+h/2}f({\bm{x}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}&&\text{for }i=2,\ldots,n_{\bm{x}}-1,\\
&\frac{2}{h}\int_{{\bm{x}}_{n_{\bm{x}}}-h/2}^{{\bm{x}}_{n_{\bm{x}}}}f({\bm{x}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}&&\text{for }i=n_{\bm{x}}.\end{aligned}\right.

Analogously, the vector
\mathrm{g}^{h}=(\mathrm{g}_{i}^{h})\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{{\bm{x}}}}
is defined. Then, the matrix
{\bm{W}}
is diagonal and given by
{\bm{W}}=\text{diag}(h/2,h,\ldots,h,h/2)
.

2)

For the parabolic problem in Example
2.1.7
, it was already argued that two natural choices for the underlying infinite-dimensional function space are given by
H=L^{2}(\Omega)
and
V=H^{1}(\Omega)
. We apply a standard piecewise linear
finite element (FE) discretization
with
m\in\mathbb{N}
degrees of freedom; cf., e.g.,
[
BS08
,
Tho06
]
. Let
\{\varphi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\subset H^{1}(\Omega)
denote the FE piecewise ansatz functions where
\varphi_{i}
takes the value one on the
i
-th node of the grid and zero on all other nodes. Then the discretized FE space is given by the linear hull

\displaystyle X^{h}=\bigg\{\varphi\in V\,\Big|\,\varphi({\bm{x}})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{v}_{i}\varphi_{i}({\bm{x}})\text{ for }\mathrm{v}=(\mathrm{v}_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\text{ and }{\bm{x}}\in\Omega\bigg\}.

We introduce the
mass matrix

{\bm{M}}=((M_{ij}))\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
with the elements
M_{ij}=\langle\varphi_{j},\varphi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
for
1\leq i,j\leq m
, and the
stiffness matrix

{\bm{S}}=((S_{ij}))\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
with the elements
S_{ij}=\langle\varphi_{i},\varphi_{j}\rangle_{V}
. Note that both
{\bm{M}}
and
{\bm{S}}
are symmetric and positive definite. Furthermore, the
H
- and
V
-inner product of two FE function

\displaystyle v({\bm{x}})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{v}_{i}\varphi_{i}({\bm{x}}),\quad w({\bm{x}})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{w}_{i}\varphi_{i}({\bm{x}}),\quad{\bm{x}}\in\Omega,~\mathrm{v}_{i},\mathrm{w}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}

can be described by

\displaystyle{\langle v,w\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathrm{v}_{i}M_{ij}\mathrm{w}_{j}=\mathrm{v}^{\top}{\bm{M}}\mathrm{w}=:{\langle\mathrm{v},\mathrm{w}\rangle}_{\bm{M}},

\displaystyle{\langle v,w\rangle}_{V}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathrm{v}_{i}S_{ij}\mathrm{w}_{j}=\mathrm{v}^{\top}{\bm{S}}\mathrm{w}=:{\langle\mathrm{v},\mathrm{w}\rangle}_{\bm{S}}

with the coefficient vectors
\mathrm{v}=(\mathrm{v}_{i}),\,\mathrm{w}=(\mathrm{w}_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Both
{\bm{M}}
and
{\bm{S}}
are possible choices for the inner product matrix
{\bm{W}}
.

3)

For the elliptic problem in Example
2.1.8
, we may also utilize the matrices
{\bm{M}}
and
{\bm{S}}
from part 2). The only difference is that the underlying function space is now given by
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C})
, so we have to allow complex vectors
\mathrm{v},\mathrm{w}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
as FE coefficients.

Both for the parabolic and the elliptic problem considered in 2) and 3), we utilize a Galerkin discretization to project the infinite-dimensional PDEs to the FE space, which will be explained in detail in Section
3.4
. This gives us ansatz fuctions
y^{h}(t)
for all times
t\in(0,T)
in the parabolic case and
p^{h}(f)\in X^{h}
for all frequencies
f\in\mathscr{F}
in the elliptic case. Let
\mathrm{y}(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\mathrm{p}(f)\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
denote their coefficient vectors. With the given discretizations of
(0,T)
and
\mathscr{F}
in Examples
2.1.7
and
2.1.8
, this leads to data trajectories
\mathrm{y}(t_{j})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\mathrm{p}(f_{j})\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
for
j=1,...,n
. The discrete versions of the associated POD problems are then given by

\displaystyle\min\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\Big|\mathrm{y}(t_{j})-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle\mathrm{y}(t_{j}),\mathrm{v}_{i}\rangle}_{{\bm{W}}}\,\mathrm{v}_{i}\Big|_{{\bm{W}}}^{2}\text{ s.t. }\{\mathrm{v}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\text{ and }{\langle\mathrm{v}_{i},\mathrm{v}_{j}\rangle}_{{\bm{W}}}=\delta_{ij},~1\leq i,j\leq\ell,

and

\displaystyle\min\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\Big|\mathrm{p}(f_{j})-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle\mathrm{p}(f_{j}),\mathrm{v}_{i}\rangle}_{{\bm{W}}}\,\mathrm{v}_{i}\Big|_{{\bm{W}}}^{2}\text{ s.t. }\{\mathrm{v}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset\mathbb{C}^{m}\text{ and }{\langle\mathrm{v}_{i},\mathrm{v}_{j}\rangle}_{{\bm{W}}}=\delta_{ij},~1\leq i,j\leq\ell.

In both cases,
{\bm{W}}
denotes either
{\bm{M}}
or
{\bm{S}}
and
\mathrm{v}_{i}
is the coordinate vector of the
i
-th POD basis vector
\psi_{i}\in X_{h}
, which lives in the FE space
X^{h}
.

As in Section
2.1.7
we introduce the snapshot matrix
{\bm{Y}}=[y_{1}\,|\ldots\,|\,y_{n}]\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
with rank
d^{n}\leq\min(m,n)
. Moreover, we define the diagonal matrix
{\bm{D}}=\mathrm{diag}\,(\alpha_{1}^{n},

\ldots,\alpha_{n}^{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
. We find that

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{R}^{n}\psi\big)_{i}

\displaystyle=\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\langle\psi,y_{j}\rangle}_{W}\,y_{j}\Big)_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{l=1}^{m}\sum_{\nu=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}^{n}\psi_{l}W_{l\nu}\overline{Y}_{\nu j}Y_{ij}

\displaystyle=\big({\bm{Y}}{\bm{D}}{\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}{\bm{W}}\psi\big)_{i}\quad\text{for all }\psi\in\mathbb{C}^{m}\text{ and }1\leq i\leq m.

Consequently, (
2.1.10
) leads to the eigenvalue problem

{\bm{Y}}{\bm{D}}{\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}{\bm{W}}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n},\quad\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{n}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{d^{n}}^{n}>\hat{\lambda}_{d^{n}+1}^{n}=\ldots=\hat{\lambda}_{m}^{n}=0.

(2.1.38)

Due to the fact that
{\bm{W}}
is a Hermitian positive-definite matrix, it can be decomposed as
{\bm{W}}={\bm{Q}}{\bm{B}}{\bm{Q}}^{\mathsf{H}}
, where the diagonal matrix
{\bm{B}}=\mathrm{diag}\,(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
contains the eigenvalues
\beta_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\beta_{m}>0
of
{\bm{W}}
and
{\bm{Q}}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}
is a unitary matrix, i.e.
{\bm{Q}}^{-1}={\bm{Q}}^{\mathsf{H}}
holds. We have

\displaystyle{\bm{W}}^{r}={\bm{Q}}{\bm{B}}^{r}{\bm{Q}}^{\mathsf{H}}\quad\text{for }r\in\mathbb{R},

with
{\bm{B}}^{r}=\mathrm{diag}\,(\beta_{1}^{r},\ldots,\beta_{m}^{r})
. Note that
({\bm{W}}^{r})^{-1}={\bm{W}}^{-r}
and
{\bm{W}}^{r+s}={\bm{W}}^{r}{\bm{W}}^{s}
for
r,\,s\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle{\langle\psi,\phi\rangle}_{\bm{W}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{\top}\overline{{\bm{W}}\phi}=\psi^{\top}\overline{{\bm{Q}}{\bm{B}}{\bm{Q}}^{\mathsf{H}}\phi}=\psi^{\top}\overline{{\bm{Q}}}{\bm{B}}^{1/2}{\bm{Q}}^{\top}\overline{{\bm{Q}}{\bm{B}}^{1/2}{\bm{Q}}^{\mathsf{H}}\phi}

\displaystyle=\big({\bm{Q}}{\bm{B}}^{1/2}{\bm{Q}}^{\mathsf{H}}\psi\big)^{\top}\overline{{\bm{Q}}{\bm{B}}^{1/2}{\bm{Q}}^{\mathsf{H}}\phi}=\big({\bm{W}}^{1/2}\psi\big)\overline{{\bm{W}}^{1/2}\phi}={\langle{\bm{W}}^{1/2}\psi,{\bm{W}}^{1/2}\phi\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}

and
|\psi|_{W}=|{\bm{W}}^{1/2}\psi|_{2}
for
\psi,\phi\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
. Note that
{\bm{D}}^{1/2}=\mathrm{diag}\,(\alpha_{1}^{1/2},\ldots,\alpha_{n}^{1/2})
holds. Inserting
\psi_{i}^{n}={\bm{W}}^{1/2}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}
in (
2.1.38
), multiplying (
2.1.38
) by
{\bm{W}}^{1/2}
from the left and setting
\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}={\bm{W}}^{1/2}{\bm{Y}}{\bm{D}}^{1/2}
yield the symmetric
m\times m
eigenvalue problem

\displaystyle\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}^{\mathsf{H}}\psi_{i}^{n}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\psi_{i}^{n}\quad\text{for }1\leq i\leq\ell.

Note that

\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}^{\mathsf{H}}\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}={\bm{D}}^{1/2}{\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}{\bm{W}}{\bm{Y}}{\bm{D}}^{1/2}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}.

(2.1.39)

Thus, the POD basis
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell
can also be computed by the methods of snapshots as follows: First solve the symmetric
n\times n
eigenvalue problem

\displaystyle\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}^{\mathsf{H}}\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}\phi_{i}^{n}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\phi_{i}^{n},~1\leq i\leq\ell\quad\text{and}\quad{\langle\phi_{i}^{n},\phi_{j}^{n}\rangle}_{2}=\delta_{ij}\quad\text{for }1\leq i,j\leq\ell.

Then we set (by using the SVD of
\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}
)

\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}={\bm{W}}^{-1/2}\psi_{i}^{n}=\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}}\,{\bm{W}}^{-1/2}\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}\phi_{i}^{n}=\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}}\,{\bm{Y}}{\bm{D}}^{1/2}\phi_{i}^{n}\quad\text{for }1\leq i\leq\ell.

(2.1.40)

We find that

\displaystyle{\langle\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n},\hat{\psi}_{j}^{n}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}

\displaystyle=(\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n})^{\top}\overline{{\bm{W}}\hat{\psi}_{j}^{n}}=\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{n}}\,(\phi_{i}^{n})^{\top}\overline{{\bm{D}}^{1/2}{\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}{\bm{W}}{\bm{Y}}{\bm{D}}^{1/2}\phi_{j}^{n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{n}}\,{\langle\phi_{i}^{n},\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}^{\mathsf{H}}\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}\phi_{j}^{n}\rangle}_{2}=\delta_{ij}\quad\text{for }1\leq i,j\leq\ell.

Thus, the POD basis
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell
is orthonormal in
\mathbb{C}^{m}
with respect to the inner product
\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle_{\bm{W}}
. We observe from (
2.1.39
) and (
2.1.40
) that the computation of
{\bm{W}}^{1/2}
and
{\bm{W}}^{-1/2}
is not required for this last approach. For applications like Example
2.1.25
, where
{\bm{W}}
is not just a diagonal matrix, the method of snapshots turns out to be more attractive with respect to the computational costs even if
n>m
holds. It holds

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{|y_{j}|}_{\bm{W}}^{2}={\|\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}\|}_{F}^{2}\quad\text{for }j=1,\ldots,n.

(2.1.41)

Thus, we infer from (
2.1.22
) that the
relative total energy
is given as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\ell)=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}}{{\|\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}\|}_{F}^{2}}.

Let us summarize all computational steps in Algorithm
2.1.9
.

0:

Snapshots
\{y_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset\mathbb{C}^{m}
, positive weights
\{\alpha_{j}^{n}\}_{j=1}^{n}
, POD rank
\ell\leq d^{n}
, Hermitian positive-definite matrix
{\bm{W}}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}
and
flag
for the eigenvalue solver;

1:

Set
{\bm{Y}}=[y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}]\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
and
{\bm{D}}=\mathrm{diag}\,(\alpha_{1}^{n},\ldots,\alpha_{n}^{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
;

2:

if

flag
= 0
then

3:

Determine
\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}={\bm{W}}^{1/2}{\bm{Y}}{\bm{D}}^{1/2}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
;

4:

Compute singular value decomposition
[{\bm{\Psi}},{\bm{\Sigma}},{\bm{\Phi}}]=\mathrm{svd}\,(\tilde{{\bm{Y}}})
;

5:

Set
\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}={\bm{W}}^{-1/2}{\bm{\Psi}}_{\cdot,i}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
and
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}=\Sigma_{ii}^{2}
for
i=1,\ldots,\ell
;

6:

Compute
\mathcal{E}(\ell)=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\lambda_{i}/{\|\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}\|}_{F}^{2}
;

7:

else

if

flag
= 1
then

8:

Determine
{\bm{R}}={\bm{W}}^{1/2}{\bm{Y}}{\bm{D}}{\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}{\bm{W}}^{1/2}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}
;

9:

Compute eigenvalue decomposition
[{\bm{\Psi}},{\bm{\Lambda}}]=\mathrm{eig}\,({\bm{R}})
;

10:

Set
\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}={\bm{W}}^{-1/2}{\bm{\Psi}}_{\cdot,i}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
and
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}=\Lambda_{ii}
for
i=1,\ldots,\ell
;

11:

Compute
\mathcal{E}(\ell)=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\lambda_{i}/{\|\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}\|}_{F}^{2}
;

12:

else

if

flag
= 2
then

13:

Determine
{\bm{K}}={\bm{D}}^{1/2}{\bm{Y}}{\bm{W}}{\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}{\bm{D}}^{1/2}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
;

14:

Compute eigenvalue decomposition
[{\bm{\Phi}},{\bm{\Lambda}}]=\mathrm{eig}\,({\bm{K}})
;

15:

Set
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}=\Lambda_{ii}
and
\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}={\bm{Y}}{\bm{D}}^{1/2}{\bm{\Phi}}_{\cdot,i}/\Lambda_{ii}^{1/2}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
for
i=1,\ldots,\ell
;

16:

Compute
\mathcal{E}(\ell)=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\lambda_{i}/{\|\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}\|}_{F}^{2}
;

17:

end

if

18:

return
POD basis
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
, eigenvalues
\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
and ratio
\mathcal{E}(\ell)
;

Example 2.1.26
.

We want to apply all three strategies of Algorithm
2.1.9
to Example
2.1.7
. In addition to the temporal discretization mentioned in that example, the infinite-dimensional state equation (
1.2.1
) is discretized using linear finite elements, thus replacing the state space
V=H^{1}(\Omega)
by a linear space
V^{m}\subset V
of dimension
m\in\mathbb{N}
. This discretization will be carried out in detail in Section
3.4
, so we do not go into more detail right now. The important part is that we have different accuracy levels indicated by the numbers
n
for the temporal discretization and
m
for the spatial discretization. Since the three strategies in Algorithm
2.1.9
solve different kinds of decomposition problems, we would like to compare their computational efficiency for three different sizes of
m
and
n
. In particular, we choose
n\in\{100,200,400\}
and
m\in\{765,1319,2999\}
and apply all three strategies to the problem using
K=20
different trajectories, exactly as in Problem
2.1.7
. We fix the basis length at
\ell=5
. Table
2.1.9
shows the results.

Example

. CPU-times for the POD basis computation by the three different strategies from Algorithm
2.1.9
. All times are in seconds, with computations carried out sequentally on a system with 3.5GHz CPU and 16GB RAM.

We can immediately tell that computation times for
flag=1
depend mostly on
m
while those for
flag=2
depend on
n
. This makes sense since we mostly have to solve eigenvalue problems of size
(nK)\times(nK)
and
m\times m
in both respectively. This underlines the importance of using the method of snapshots (
flag=2
) only for comparably small numbers of
nK
. Alternatively, the singular value decomposition in the case of
flag=0
offers a compromise in terms of efficiency, never giving excessively high computation times but being beaten for large quotients of
(nK)/m
by
flag=1
and for small quotients of
(nK)/m
by
flag=2
.
\blacklozenge

2.1.10
POD for finite-dimensional dynamical systems

In Example
2.1.25
-b), we have already encountered a finite-dimensional dynamical system which arose from a semi-discretization of a parabolic PDE. In this section, we will study how POD can be applied to these types of systems. We will focus on linear systems where the linear operator and the inhomogeneity may additionally depend on a parameter from a compact set, which will result in
K>1
data trajectories for the POD problem.

Let us choose
X=\mathbb{C}^{m}
endowed with the weighted inner product (
2.1.37
). Assume that
\mathscr{N}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{p}}
is a compact set and
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{N}
. For
T>0
we consider the parametrized linear initial value problem

\dot{y}(t)={\bm{A}}({\bm{\nu}})y(t)+f(t;{\bm{\nu}})\quad\text{for }t\in(0,T],\quad y(0)=y_{\circ},

(2.1.42)

where
y_{\circ}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
is a given initial condition and
{\bm{A}}({\bm{\nu}})\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}
is a coefficient matrix, which depends on the parameter
{\bm{\nu}}\in\mathscr{D}
. Moreover, the parameter-dependent mapping
f(\cdot\,;{\bm{\nu}}):[0,T]\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
is continuous for any parameter
{\bm{\nu}}\in\mathscr{N}
. It is well-known that for any
{\bm{\nu}}
there exists a unique (classical) solution
y=y({\bm{\nu}})\in C^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{C}^{m})\cap C([0,T];\mathbb{C}^{m})
to (
2.1.42
). Recall that the solution
y
to (
2.1.42
) is given by the integral representation

\displaystyle y(t;{\bm{\nu}})=e^{{\bm{A}}({\bm{\nu}})t}y_{\circ}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{{\bm{A}}({\bm{\nu}})(t-s)}f(s;{\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}s\quad\text{for }{\bm{\nu}}\in\mathscr{N}

with
e^{{\bm{A}}({\bm{\nu}})t}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}t^{n}{\bm{A}}({\bm{\nu}})^{n}/n!
is the exponential of matrix
{\bm{A}}({\bm{\nu}})
; cf.
[
Paz83
]
.

Example 2.1.27
.

For the spatial domain
\Omega=(0,1)\subset\mathbb{R}
we define the space-time cylinder
Q=(0,T)\times\Omega
. The parameter set
\mathscr{D}
is given by the three-dimensional rectangular domain

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}=\big[\upnu_{\mathsf{a}1},\upnu_{\mathsf{b}1}\big]\times\big[\upnu_{\mathsf{a}2},\upnu_{\mathsf{b}2}\big]\times\big[\upnu_{\mathsf{a}3},\upnu_{\mathsf{b}3}\big]=:\big[{\bm{\nu}}_{\mathsf{a}},{\bm{\nu}}_{\mathsf{b}}\big]\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}

with
{\bm{\nu}}_{\mathsf{a}}=(\upnu_{\mathsf{a}i})_{1\leq i\leq 3},{\bm{\nu}}_{\mathsf{b}}=(\upnu_{\mathsf{b}i})_{1\leq i\leq 3}\in\mathscr{N}
and
0\leq\upnu_{\mathsf{a}i}\leq\upnu_{\mathsf{b}i}
for
i=1,2,3
. For any
{\bm{\nu}}=(\upnu_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 3}\in\mathscr{N}
let us consider the following one-dimensional advection-diffusion equation:

\displaystyle y_{t}(t,{\bm{x}})=\upnu_{1}c({\bm{x}})y_{{\bm{x}}{\bm{x}}}(t,{\bm{x}})+\upnu_{2}v({\bm{x}})y_{\bm{x}}(t,{\bm{x}})+\upnu_{3}f(t,{\bm{x}}),

\displaystyle(t,{\bm{x}})\in Q,

(2.1.43)

\displaystyle y(t,0)=y(t,1)=0,

\displaystyle t\in(0,T],

\displaystyle y(0,{\bm{x}})=y_{\circ}({\bm{x}}),

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\bm{x}}\in\Omega,

where
c,v,y_{\circ}
are given functions in
C(\overline{\Omega})
with
c({\bm{x}})\geq c_{0}>0
for all
{\bm{x}}\in\Omega
and a given inhomogeneity
f\in C(\overline{Q})
. To solve (
2.1.43
) numerically we apply a standard finite difference approximation for the spatial variable
x
; cf., e.g.,
[
Str04
]
. To simplify the presentation we suppose the spatial grid is equidistant, i.e.,
x_{i}=ih
for
0\leq i\leq m+1
with a step size
h=1/(m+1)
. Setting
\mathrm{c}_{i}=c({\bm{x}}_{i})
,
\mathrm{v}_{i}=v({\bm{x}}_{i})
,
\mathrm{y}_{\circ i}=y_{\circ}({\bm{x}}_{i})
and
\mathrm{f}_{i}(t)=f(t,{\bm{x}}_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq m
,
y\in\mathbb{R}
and
{\bm{\nu}}\in\mathscr{N}
, we consider instead of (
2.1.43
) the problem

\displaystyle y_{t}(t,{\bm{x}}_{i})

\displaystyle=\upnu_{1}\mathrm{c}_{i}y_{xx}(t,{\bm{x}}_{i})+\upnu_{2}\mathrm{v}_{i}y_{\bm{x}}(t,{\bm{x}}_{i})+\upnu_{3}\mathrm{f}_{i}(t),

\displaystyle\hskip-7.11317ptt\in(0,T],1\leq i\leq m,

(2.1.44a)

\displaystyle y(t,{\bm{x}}_{0})

\displaystyle=y(t,{\bm{x}}_{m+1})=0,

\displaystyle\hskip-7.11317ptt\in(0,T],

(2.1.44b)

\displaystyle y(0,{\bm{x}}_{i})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{y}_{\circ i},

\displaystyle\hskip-7.11317pt1\leq i\leq m.

(2.1.44c)

The first and second partial derivatives of
y
with respect to
x
are discretized by centered difference quotients of second-order. Then we have (cf.
[
Str04
]
)

\displaystyle y_{{\bm{x}}{\bm{x}}}(t,{\bm{x}}_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{y(t,{\bm{x}}_{i+1})-2y(t,{\bm{x}}_{i})+y(t,{\bm{x}}_{i-1})}{h^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\big(h^{2}\big),

(2.1.45)

\displaystyle y_{\bm{x}}(t,{\bm{x}}_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{y(t,{\bm{x}}_{i+1})-y(t,{\bm{x}}_{i-1})}{2h}+\mathcal{O}\big(h^{2}\big)

for
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
and
t\in[0,T]
provided the fourth partial derivative of
v
with respect to
x
exists for any
t\in[0,T]
and is continuous on
\overline{Q}
. Let us denote by
\mathrm{y}_{i}:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
the numerical approximation for
y(\cdot\,,{\bm{x}}_{i})
for
i=1,\ldots,m
. From (
2.1.44b
) we get
\mathrm{y}_{0}=\mathrm{y}_{m+1}=0
on
[0,T]
so that we obtain the following ordinary differential equations for the time-dependent functions
\mathrm{y}_{i}
:

\left\{\begin{aligned} \dot{\mathrm{y}}_{1}(t)&=a_{11}({\bm{\nu}})\mathrm{y}_{1}(t)+a_{12}({\bm{\nu}})\mathrm{y}_{2}(t)+\upnu_{3}\mathrm{f}_{1}(t),\\
\dot{\mathrm{y}}_{i}(t)&=a_{i,i-1}({\bm{\nu}})\mathrm{y}_{i-1}(t)+a_{ii}({\bm{\nu}})\mathrm{y}_{i}(t)+a_{i,i+1}({\bm{\nu}})\mathrm{y}_{i+1}(t)+\upnu_{3}\mathrm{f}_{i}(t),\quad i=2,\ldots,m-1,\\
\dot{\mathrm{y}}_{m}(t)&=a_{m,m-1}({\bm{\nu}})\mathrm{y}_{m-1}(t)+a_{mm}({\bm{\nu}})\mathrm{y}_{m}(t)+\upnu_{3}\mathrm{f}_{m}(t)\end{aligned}\right.

(2.1.46a)

with the coefficients

\displaystyle a_{i,i-1}({\bm{\nu}})=\frac{\upnu_{1}\mathrm{c}_{i}}{h^{2}}-\frac{\upnu_{2}\mathrm{v}_{i}}{2h},\quad a_{ii}({\bm{\nu}})=-\frac{2\upnu_{1}\mathrm{c}_{i}}{h^{2}},\quad a_{i,i+1}({\bm{\nu}})=\frac{\upnu_{1}\mathrm{c}_{i}}{h^{2}}+\frac{\upnu_{2}\mathrm{v}_{i}}{2h}.

From (
2.1.44c
) we infer the initial condition

\mathrm{y}_{i}(0)=\mathrm{y}_{\circ i}\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,m.

(2.1.46b)

Introducing the matrix
{\bm{A}}({\bm{\nu}})=((a_{ij}({\bm{\nu}})))\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
and the vectors

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}(t)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathrm{y}_{1}(t)\\
\vdots\\
\mathrm{y}_{m}(t)\end{array}\right),\quad\mathrm{f}(t)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathrm{f}_{1}(t)\\
\vdots\\
\mathrm{f}_{m}(t)\end{array}\right)\mbox{ for }t\in[0,T],\quad\mathrm{y}_{\circ}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathrm{y}_{\circ 1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathrm{y}_{\circ m}\end{array}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}

we can express (
2.1.46
) in the form

\dot{\mathrm{y}}(t)={\bm{A}}({\bm{\nu}})\mathrm{y}(t)+\upnu_{3}\mathrm{f}(t)\quad\text{for }t\in(0,T],\quad\mathrm{y}(0)=\mathrm{y}_{\circ}.

(2.1.47)

The vector
\mathrm{y}(t)
,
t\in[0,T]
, represents a function in
\Omega
evaluated at
m
grid points. Therefore, we should supply
\mathbb{R}^{m}
by a weighted inner product representing a discretized inner product in an appropriate function space. Here we choose the inner product introduced in Example
2.1.25
.
\blacklozenge

Let
0=t_{1}<t_{2}<\ldots<t_{n}=T
be a given time grid in the interval
[0,T]
, where the step sizes are given as
\delta t_{j}=t_{j}-t_{j-1}
for
j=2,\ldots,n
. We suppose that we know the solution
y=y(t_{j};{\bm{\nu}})
to (
2.1.42
) at the given time instances
t_{j}
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and for the parameters
{\bm{\nu}}_{k}\in\mathscr{N}
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,{K}\}
. Our goal is to determine a POD basis of rank
\ell\leq\min\{m,n\}
for the snapshot subspace

\displaystyle\mathscr{V}^{n}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{y_{j}^{k}=y(t_{j};{\bm{\nu}}_{k})\,\big|\,1\leq j\leq n\text{ and }1\leq k\leq K\big\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{m}.

For
1\leq k\leq K
we find that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\Big|y_{j}^{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}\,\psi_{i}\Big|_{\bm{W}}^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\Big|y(t_{j};{\bm{\nu}}^{k})-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y(t_{j};{\bm{\nu}}^{k}),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}\,\psi_{i}\Big|_{\bm{W}}^{2}

which can be interpreted as a quadrature rule for the integral

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\Big|y(t;{\bm{\nu}}^{k})-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y(t;{\bm{\nu}}^{k}),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}\,\psi_{i}\Big|_{\bm{W}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t,

provided that
\{\alpha_{j}^{n}\}_{j=1}^{n}
are chosen, e.g., as trapezoidal weights

\alpha_{1}^{n}=\frac{\delta t_{1}}{2},\quad\alpha_{j}=\frac{\delta t_{j-1}+\delta t_{j}}{2}\text{ for }j=2,\ldots,n-1,\quad\alpha_{n}=\frac{\delta t_{m}}{2}.

(2.1.48)

It is worth to mention that a choice of trapezoidal weights leads to the same order of the approximation error as the discretization of the first and second derivatives by second-order centered difference quotients in (
2.1.45
). This issue will be addressed in a more detail in Section
2.3
. Of course, other choices are also possible, e.g., Simpson weights. Now, (

P
ℓ

n

) reads

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\min\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\Big|y(t_{j};{\bm{\nu}}^{k})-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y(t_{j};{\bm{\nu}}^{k}),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}\,\psi_{i}\Big|_{\bm{W}}^{2}\\
&\hskip 1.42262pt\text{s.t. }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset\mathbb{C}^{m}\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}=\delta_{ij},~1\leq i,j\leq\ell.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.1.49)

Next we investigate (
2.1.49
). As in Section
2.1.9
let
{\bm{D}}=\mathrm{diag}\,(\alpha_{1}^{n},\ldots,\alpha_{n}^{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
. We introduce the matrices
{\bm{Y}}^{k}=[y_{1}^{k}\,|\ldots\,|\,y_{n}^{K}]\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
for
k=1,\ldots,{K}
and set
{\bm{Y}}=[{\bm{Y}}^{1}\,|\ldots\,|\,{\bm{Y}}^{k}]\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times(n{K})}
with
\mathrm{rank}\,{\bm{Y}}=d^{n}\leq\min(m,n{K})
. Then we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{n}\psi=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\langle\psi,y_{j}^{k}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}\,y_{j}^{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}{\bm{Y}}^{k}{\bm{D}}(Y^{k})^{\mathsf{H}}{\bm{W}}\psi={\bm{Y}}\tilde{{\bm{D}}}{\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}{\bm{W}}\psi

for
\psi\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
with the diagonal block matrix
\tilde{{\bm{D}}}=\mathrm{diag}(\omega_{1}^{K}{\bm{D}},\ldots,\omega_{K}^{K}{\bm{D}})\in\mathbb{R}^{(n{K})\times(n{K})}
. Hence, (
2.1.10
) corresponds to the eigenvalue problem

{\bm{Y}}\tilde{{\bm{D}}}Y^{\mathsf{H}}{\bm{W}}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n},\quad\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{n}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{d^{n}}^{n}>\hat{\lambda}_{d^{n}+1}^{n}=\ldots=\hat{\lambda}_{m}^{n}=0.

(2.1.50)

Setting
\psi_{i}^{n}={\bm{W}}^{1/2}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}
in (
2.1.50
) and multiplying by
{\bm{W}}^{1/2}
from the left yield

{\bm{W}}^{1/2}{\bm{Y}}\tilde{{\bm{D}}}{\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}W^{1/2}\psi_{i}^{n}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\psi_{i}^{n}.

(2.1.51)

Let
\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}={\bm{W}}^{1/2}{\bm{Y}}\tilde{{\bm{D}}}^{1/2}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times(n{K})}
. Using
{\bm{W}}^{\mathsf{H}}={\bm{W}}
as well as
\tilde{{\bm{D}}}^{\top}=\tilde{{\bm{D}}}
we infer from (
2.1.51
) that the POD basis
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell
is given by the symmetric
m\times m
eigenvalue problem

\displaystyle\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}^{\mathsf{H}}\psi_{i}^{n}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\psi_{i}^{n}\quad\text{for }1\leq i\leq\ell,\quad\text{and}\quad{\langle\psi_{i}^{n},\psi_{j}^{n}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}=\delta_{ij}\quad\text{for }1\leq i,j\leq\ell,

and
\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}={\bm{W}}^{-1/2}\psi_{i}^{n}
. Note that

\displaystyle\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}^{\mathsf{H}}\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}=\tilde{{\bm{D}}}^{1/2}{\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}{\bm{W}}{\bm{Y}}\tilde{{\bm{D}}}^{1/2}\in\mathbb{C}^{(n{K})\times(n{K})}.

Thus, the POD basis of rank
\ell
can also be computed by the methods of snapshots as follows: First solve the symmetric
(n{K})\times(n{K})
eigenvalue problem

\displaystyle\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}^{\mathsf{H}}\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}\phi_{i}^{n}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\phi_{i}^{n}\quad\text{for }1\leq i\leq\ell\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\langle\phi_{i}^{n},\phi_{j}^{n}\rangle}_{2}=\delta_{ij}\quad\text{for }1\leq i,j\leq\ell.

Then we set (by SVD)

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}_{i}^{n}={\bm{W}}^{-1/2}\psi_{i}^{n}=\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}}\,{\bm{W}}^{-1/2}\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}\phi_{i}^{n}=\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}}\,{\bm{Y}}\tilde{{\bm{D}}}^{1/2}\phi_{i}^{n}\quad\text{for }1\leq i\leq\ell.

In real applications the solution of (
2.1.42
) has to be approximated by utilizing a time integration method. Here, we apply the
Crank-Nicolson scheme

[
Str04
, Chapter 6.3]
and compute approximations
y_{j}=y_{j}({\bm{\nu}})\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
,
j=1,\ldots,n
, of the solution to (
2.1.42
) at the time instances
\{t_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
for
{\bm{\nu}}\in\mathscr{N}
:

\displaystyle y_{1}=y_{\circ},

(2.1.52)

\displaystyle\text{\bf for }j=2\text{ \bf to }n

\displaystyle\frac{y_{j}-y_{j-1}}{\delta t_{j}}=\frac{1}{2}\,\Big(A({\bm{\nu}})\big(y_{j-1}+y_{j}\big)+f(t_{j-1};{\bm{\nu}})+f(t_{j};{\bm{\nu}})\Big).

From (
2.1.52
) we compute approximations

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{m}\ni y_{j}^{k}=y_{j}({\bm{\nu}}_{k})\approx y(t_{j};{\bm{\nu}}_{k})\in\mathbb{C}^{m},

which serve as our snapshots in the computation of the POD basis; see Algorithm
2.1.10
. Notice that we simplify our presentation by choosing the same temporal grid for every parameter
{\bm{\nu}}
. In practise, we usually have to choose different, in particular adaptive time grids. Let us refer to the recent work
[
Grä19
,
GHV21
]
.

0:

Positive weights
\{\omega_{k}^{K}\}_{k=1}^{K}
and
\{\alpha_{j}^{n}\}_{j=1}^{n}
, POD rank
\ell\leq\min(m,n{K})
, Hermitian positive-definite matrix
{\bm{W}}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}
, parameter grid
\{{\bm{\nu}}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}\subset\mathscr{N}
and
flag
for the eigenvalue solver;

1:

for

to

{K}

do

Solve (
2.1.52
) for
y_{j}^{k}=y_{j}({\bm{\nu}}_{k})\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
,
j=1,\ldots,n
;

Set
{\bm{Y}}^{k}=[y_{1}^{k}\,|\ldots|\,y_{n}^{k}]\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
;

for

{\bm{Y}}=[{\bm{Y}}^{1}\,|\ldots|\,{\bm{Y}}^{K}]\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times(n{K})}
;

Set
{\bm{D}}=\mathrm{diag}\,(\alpha_{1}^{n},\ldots,\alpha_{n}^{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and
\tilde{{\bm{D}}}=\mathrm{diag}\,(\omega_{1}^{K}{\bm{D}},\ldots,\omega_{K}^{K}{\bm{D}})\in\mathbb{R}^{(n{K})\times(n{K})}
;

if

flag
= 0
then

Determine
\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}={\bm{W}}^{1/2}{\bm{Y}}\tilde{{\bm{D}}}^{1/2}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times(n{K})}
;

9:

Compute singular value decomposition
[{\bm{\Psi}},{\bm{\Sigma}},{\bm{\Phi}}]=\mathrm{svd}\,(\tilde{{\bm{Y}}})
;

10:

Set

and

for
i=1,\ldots,\ell
;

;

if

flag
= 1
then

[{\bm{\Psi}},{\bm{\Lambda}}]=\mathrm{eig}\,({\bm{R}})

for

= 2

2.2
The continuous variant of the POD method

In Section
2.1
we have considered the case of a finite number of snapshots
y_{1}^{k},\ldots,y_{n}^{k}\in X
for
1\leq k\leq{K}
, leading to the discrete variant of the POD method. As we have seen in Section
2.1.10
the discrete set of snapshots is in many cases an approximation of a snaphot continuum and the weights
\{\alpha_{j}^{n}\}_{j=1}^{n}
are given as quadrature weights to approximate an integral inner product in this case.

So let us suppose that we are given a parameter set
\mathscr{D}
, which is a closed and bounded subset of
\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{p}}
with
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{N}
, and trajectories
y^{k}\in C(\mathscr{D};X)
,
1\leq k\leq{K}
. So far, for the discrete variant of the POD method, we have assumed a finite parameter grid

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}^{n}=\{{\bm{\mu}}_{1},\ldots,{\bm{\mu}}_{n}\}\subset\mathscr{D}

and an approximation of the snapshots by
y_{j}^{k}=y^{k}({\bm{\mu}}_{j})\in X
or
y_{j}^{k}\approx y^{k}({\bm{\mu}}_{j})\in X
. Then the snapshot subspace
\mathscr{V}^{n}
of dimension
d^{n}
introduced in (
2.1.1
) depends on the chosen grid points
\{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
. Consequently, the operator
\mathcal{R}^{n}
defined in (
2.1.9
) and thus the POD basis
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell
as well as the corresponding eigenvalues
\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
depend also on the grid
\mathscr{D}^{n}
(which has already been indicated by the superindex
n
). In this sense, enlarging the number
n
of snapshots
\{y_{j}^{k}\}_{j=1}^{n}
and weights
\{\alpha_{j}^{n}\}_{j=1}^{n}
corresponds to a finer approximation of the continuous snapshots and the respective integrals.

We now study the POD method when taking the continuous snapshots
y^{k}\in C(\mathscr{D};X)
,
1\leq k\leq{K}
, instead of the discretized ones, leading to the continuous variant of the POD method. In detail, the discrete trajectory set
\mathcal{V}^{n}
from (
2.1.1
) is now replaced by a continuous
X
-valued set.

In this section we show that the theory from the discrete variant can be adapted to the continuous case and that the POD basis can be obtained analogously to the discrete case.

Later in Section 2.4 we will then investigate how the grid points
\{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
and the weights
\{\alpha_{j}^{n}\}_{j=1}^{n}
have to be chosen to obtain the convergence of the discrete POD basis
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
to the continuous POD basis
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
as
n\to\infty
, i.e., when the parameter grid is being increasingly refined.

2.2.1
Problem formulation and main result

As mentioned above, we suppose that
\mathscr{D}
is a closed and bounded subset of
\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{p}}
with
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{N}
. In Section
2.2.3
we will study the special case
\mathscr{D}=[0,T]
in more detail. Suppose that we have trajectories or
snapshots

y^{k}\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D};X)
,
1\leq k\leq{K}
. Let us now introduce the
snapshot space
by

\displaystyle\mathscr{V}=\Bigg\{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\phi^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\,\Big|\,\phi^{k}\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D})\text{ for }1\leq k\leq K\Bigg\}\subset X

with the dimension

\displaystyle d=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\dim\mathscr{V}&&\text{if }\dim\mathscr{V}\text{ is finite},\\
&\infty&&\text{otherwise}.\end{aligned}\right.

Instead of Problem
2.1.2
we consider the following problem in this section:

Problem 2.2.1

(Continuous POD method)
.

For any
\ell\leq d
, the continuous method of POD consists in determining a POD basis of rank
\ell
which minimizes the mean square error between the trajectories
y^{k}
and the corresponding
\ell
-th partial Fourier sums on average in the closed, bounded set
\mathscr{D}
:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\min\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\Big\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y^{k}({\bm{\mu}}),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{X}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\\
&\hskip 1.42262pt\text{s.t. }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset X\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{X}=\delta_{ij},~1\leq i,j\leq\ell.\end{aligned}\right.

(
\mathbf{P}^{\ell}
)

Definition 2.2.2
.

An optimal solution
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
to Problem
2.2.1
is called a POD basis of rank
\ell
.

Remark 2.2.3
.

Analogous to (

^
P

ℓ

n

) we can – instead of (

P
ℓ

) – consider the problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\max\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\big|{\langle y^{k}({\bm{\mu}}),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\\
&\hskip 1.42262pt\text{s.t. }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset X\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{X}=\delta_{ij},~1\leq i,j\leq\ell;\end{aligned}\right.

(
\mathbf{\hat{P}}^{\ell}
)

cf. Remark
2.1.13
. A solution to (

P
ℓ

) solves also (

^
P

ℓ

) and vice versa.
\blacksquare

Main result:
In Theorem
2.2.7
we will show that for every
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
a solution
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
to (

P
ℓ

) and to (

^
P

ℓ

) is characterized by the eigenvalue problem

\mathcal{R}\hat{\psi}_{i}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}\hat{\psi}_{i},\quad 1\leq i\leq\ell,

(2.2.1)

where
\hat{\lambda}_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{d}>0
and the linear integral operator
\mathcal{R}:X\to X
is given as

\mathcal{R}\psi=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}{\langle\psi,y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{X}\,y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\quad\text{for }\psi\in X.

(2.2.2)

2.2.2
Derivation of the main result

To proof our main result we define the linear operator
\mathcal{Y}:L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K})\to X
by

\mathcal{Y}\phi=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\phi^{k}({\bm{\mu}})y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\quad\text{for }\phi=(\phi^{1},\ldots,\phi^{K})\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K}).

(2.2.3)

Notice that
\mathrm{ran}\,(\mathcal{Y})=\mathscr{V}
holds true. The following result is proved in Section
2.5.2
.

Lemma 2.2.4
.

Let
y^{k}\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D};X)
,
1\leq k\leq K
, be given snapshot trajectories.

1)

The operator
\mathcal{Y}
is bounded.

2)

The adjoint
\mathcal{Y}^{\star}:X\to L^{2}(\mathscr{D};X)
is given by

(\mathcal{Y}^{\star}\psi)({\bm{\mu}})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\langle\psi,y^{1}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{X}\\
\vdots\\
{\langle\psi,y^{K}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{X}\\
\end{array}\right)\quad\text{for }\psi\in X\text{ and }{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathscr{D}\text{ a.e.,}

(2.2.4)

where
a.e. stands for almost everywhere and the (weighted) inner product in
L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K})
is given as

\displaystyle{\langle\phi,\tilde{\phi}\rangle}_{L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K})}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\,\int_{\mathscr{D}}\phi^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\tilde{\phi}^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\quad\text{for }\phi,\tilde{\phi}\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K}).

Furthermore,
\mathcal{Y}^{\star}
is compact.

From Lemma
2.2.4
we infer the next result, which is proved in Section
2.5.2
.

Corollary 2.2.5
.

Let
y^{k}\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D};X)
,
1\leq k\leq K
, be given snapshot trajectories.

1)

The operator
\mathcal{R}
introduced in (
2.2.2
) is is given as
\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\star}
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{R}
is compact, self-adjoint and non-negative.

2)

For every
{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathscr{D}
the linear operator
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{Y}^{\star}\mathcal{Y}:L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K})\to L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K})
is given as

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{K}\phi\big)({\bm{\mu}})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}{\langle y^{k}({\bm{\nu}}),y^{1}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{X}\phi^{k}({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}\\
\vdots\\
\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}{\langle y^{k}({\bm{\nu}}),y^{K}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{X}\phi^{k}({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}\end{array}\right)\quad\text{for }\phi\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K}).

Moreover,
\mathcal{K}
is compact.

Remark 2.2.6
.

In (
2.1.17
) we have introduced the linear and compact operator
\mathcal{Y}^{n}:\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}\to X
, which can be interpreted as an approximation of the operator
\mathcal{Y}
defined in (
2.2.3
) provided

\displaystyle\int_{\mathscr{D}}\phi^{k}({\bm{\mu}})y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\approx\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\Phi_{kj}y^{k}_{j}\quad\text{for }1\leq k\leq{K}.

Since
\Phi^{k}({\bm{\mu}}_{j})
as well as
y^{k}({\bm{\mu}}_{j})
might not be well-defined for a given
\Phi^{k}\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C})
and
y^{k}\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D};X)
, we set

\displaystyle\Phi_{kj}=\frac{1}{|\mathscr{M}_{j}|}\int_{\mathscr{M}_{j}}\phi^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\quad\text{and}\quad y^{k}_{j}=\frac{1}{|\mathscr{M}_{j}|}\int_{\mathscr{M}_{j}}y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}},

where
\mathscr{M}_{1},\ldots,\mathscr{M}_{n}\subset\mathscr{D}
are open sets with non-zero measure
|\mathscr{M}_{j}|
for all
j=1,\ldots,n
, such that
\cup_{j=1}^{n}\mathscr{M}_{j}=\mathscr{D}
and
{\bm{\mu}}_{j}\in\mathscr{M}_{j}
. We will address this issue in Section
2.3
in more detail.
\blacksquare

Recall the definition of the index set
\mathbb{I}
in Section
2.1.1
. In the next theorem we formulate how the solution to (

P
ℓ

) and (

^
P

ℓ

) can be found. The proof is given in Section
2.5.2
.

Theorem 2.2.7
.

Let
y^{k}\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D};X)
be given trajectories for
1\leq k\leq{K}
. Suppose that the linear operator
\mathcal{R}
is defined by (
2.2.2
). Then there exist non-negative eigenvalues
\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
and associated orthonormal eigenfunctions
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
satisfying

\mathcal{R}\hat{\psi}_{i}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}\hat{\psi}_{i},\hskip 5.69054pt\left\{\begin{aligned} &\hat{\lambda}_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{d}>\hat{\lambda}_{d+1}=\ldots=0&&\text{if }d<\infty,\\
&\hat{\lambda}_{1}\geq\hat{\lambda}_{2}\geq\ldots\text{ and }\lim_{i\to\infty}\hat{\lambda}_{i}=0&&\text{if }d=\infty.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.2.5)

For every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
(with
\ell\leq d
if
d
is finite) the first
\ell
eigenfunctions
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
solve (

P
ℓ

) and (

^
P

ℓ

). Moreover, the value of the objectives evaluated at the optimal solution
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
satisfies

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\Big\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y^{k}({\bm{\mu}}),\hat{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\hat{\psi}_{i}\Big\|_{X}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}=\sum_{i>\ell}\hat{\lambda}_{i}

(2.2.6)

and

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\big|{\langle y^{k}({\bm{\mu}}),\hat{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\hat{\lambda}_{i},

(2.2.7)

respectively.

We obtain also a result similar to Lemma
2.1.12
-1). Its proof is given in Section
2.5.2
.

Lemma 2.2.8
.

Let
y^{k}\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D};X)
be given trajectories for
1\leq k\leq{K}
. Suppose that the linear operator
\mathcal{R}
is defined by (
2.2.2
). Moreover, let
\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
and
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
be the non-negative eigenvalues and associated orthonormal eigenfunctions, respectively, satisfying (
2.2.5
).

1)

Then we have

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\big|{\langle y^{k}({\bm{\mu}}),\hat{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}={\langle\mathcal{R}\hat{\psi}_{i},\hat{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{X}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}\quad\text{for any }i\in\mathbb{I}.

(2.2.8)

In particular, if
d<\infty
holds it follows that

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\big|{\langle y^{k}({\bm{\mu}}),\hat{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}=0\quad\text{for all }i>d.

(2.2.9)

2)

The identity

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}{\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\|}_{X}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{i=1}^{d}\hat{\lambda}_{i}&&\text{if }\mathrm{rank}(\mathcal{R})=d<\infty,\\
&\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\hat{\lambda}_{i}&&\text{otherwise}\end{aligned}\right.

(2.2.10)

holds.

Remark 2.2.9

(Singular value decomposition)
.

Suppose that
y^{k}\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D};X)
holds. We have introduced the linear operator
\mathcal{Y}:L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{R}^{K})\to X
in (
2.2.3
). Recall that the linear operators
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{*}
and
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{Y}^{*}\mathcal{Y}
are compact, non-negative and self-adjoint. Due to Theorem
2.2.7
there exist non-negative eigenvalues
\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
and associated orthonormal eigenfunctions
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
satisfying (
2.2.5
). Furthermore, there is a sequence
\{\hat{\phi}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
of orthonormal eigenfunctions such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\hat{\phi}_{i}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}\hat{\phi}_{i}\quad\text{for }i\in\mathbb{I}.

We set
\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}=\{s\in\mathbb{R}\,|\,s\geq 0\}
and
\hat{\sigma}_{i}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{1/2}
for
i\in\mathbb{I}
. The sequence
\{\hat{\sigma}_{i},\hat{\phi}_{i},\hat{\psi}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}\times L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{R}^{K})\times X
can be interpreted as a
singular value decomposition
of the mapping
\mathcal{Y}
. In fact, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}\hat{\phi}_{i}=\hat{\sigma}_{i}\hat{\psi}_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{Y}^{\star}\hat{\psi}_{i}=\hat{\sigma}_{i}\hat{\phi}_{i}\quad\text{for }i\in\mathbb{I}.

Since
\hat{\sigma}_{i}>0
holds for
1=1\ldots,d
, we have
\hat{\phi}_{i}=\mathcal{Y}^{\star}\hat{\psi}_{i}/\hat{\sigma}_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,d
. For the specific case
X=\mathbb{C}^{m}
,
K=1
,
\omega_{1}^{K}=1
and one trajectory
x\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{m})
we find

\displaystyle\hat{\psi}_{i}=\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{i}}\,\mathcal{Y}\hat{\phi}_{i}=\int_{\mathscr{D}}\bigg(\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{i}}\,\hat{\phi}_{i}({\bm{\mu}})\bigg)y({\bm{\mu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,\ell,

which can be interpreted as the continuous variant of the
method of snapshots
; cf. Remark
2.1.23
.
\blacksquare

2.2.3
POD for finite-dimensional dynamical systems

In this section we continue the discussion of Section
2.1.10
by introducing a continuous version of the POD method for the given initial-value problem (
2.1.42
). Let
X=\mathbb{C}^{m}
be endowed with the weighted inner product (
2.1.37
). In the context of Section
2.2.1
we choose
\mathscr{D}=[0,T]
. We write
t
instead of
{\bm{\mu}}
. Then (

P
ℓ

) has the form

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\min\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\Big\|y(t;{\bm{\nu}}_{k})-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y(t;{\bm{\nu}}_{k}),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{\bm{W}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 1.42262pt\text{s.t. }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset\mathbb{C}^{m}\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}=\delta_{ij},~1\leq i,j\leq\ell.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.2.11)

We introduce the matrix
{\bm{R}}=((R_{ij}))\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}
with the elements

\displaystyle R_{ij}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\mathrm{y}^{k}_{i}(t;{\bm{\nu}}_{k})\overline{\mathrm{y}^{k}_{j}(t;{\bm{\nu}}_{k})}\,\mathrm{d}t\quad\text{for }1\leq i,j\leq m,

where
\mathrm{y}^{k}_{i}(t;{\bm{\nu}}_{k})
stands for the
i
-th component of the vector
y^{k}(t;{\bm{\nu}}_{k})\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
. From

\displaystyle\overline{R}_{ij}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\overline{\mathrm{y}^{k}_{i}(t;{\bm{\nu}}_{k})}\mathrm{y}^{k}_{j}(t;{\bm{\nu}}_{k})\,\mathrm{d}t=R_{ji}\quad\text{for }1\leq i,j\leq m,

it follows that
{\bm{R}}^{\mathsf{H}}={\bm{R}}
holds true. Let
\psi\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
be arbitrarily chosen and
i\in\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{R}\psi\big)_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}{\langle\psi,y(t;{\bm{\nu}}_{k})\rangle}_{\bm{W}}\mathrm{y}^{k}_{i}(t;{\bm{\nu}}_{k})\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{l=1}^{m}\psi_{l}W_{lj}\int_{0}^{T}\mathrm{y}^{k}_{i}(t;{\bm{\nu}}_{k})\overline{\mathrm{y}_{j}(t;{\bm{\nu}}_{k})}\,\mathrm{d}t=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{l=1}^{m}R_{ij}W_{jl}\psi_{l}

\displaystyle=\big({\bm{R}}{\bm{W}}\psi\big)_{i}.

Consequently, the eigenvalue problem (
2.2.5
) reads

{\bm{R}}{\bm{W}}\hat{\psi}_{i}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}\hat{\psi}_{i},\hskip 5.69054pt\hat{\lambda}_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{d}>\hat{\lambda}_{d+1}=\ldots=0.

(2.2.12)

Setting
\psi_{i}={\bm{W}}^{1/2}\hat{\psi}_{i}
and multiplying (
2.2.12
) by
{\bm{W}}^{1/2}
from the left we find

\tilde{{\bm{R}}}\psi_{i}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}\psi_{i},\hskip 5.69054pt\hat{\lambda}_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{d}>\hat{\lambda}_{d+1}=\ldots=0,

(2.2.13)

with
\tilde{{\bm{R}}}={\bm{W}}^{1/2}{\bm{R}}{\bm{W}}^{1/2}
. Since
\tilde{{\bm{R}}}^{\mathsf{H}}=\tilde{{\bm{R}}}
is valid, (
2.2.13
) is a Hermitian eigenvalue problem in
\mathbb{C}^{m}
.

2.3
Perturbation analysis for the POD basis

So far, we have introduced two variants of the POD method: In Section
2.1
, the snapshots
\{y_{i}^{k}\}_{i=1}^{n}
are given over a discrete index set
\{1,...,n\}
for
1\leq k\leq{K}
, which is why the corresponding problem (

P
ℓ

n

) is also called the
discrete POD method
; cf. Problem
2.1.2
. Meanwhile, a continuous snapshot trajectory
\{y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})~|~{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathscr{D}\}
with
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{p}}
occurred in Section
2.2
along with the corresponding
continuous POD method
; cf. Problem
2.2.1
. It was shown that the solutions to these problems are given by orthonormal eigenvectors
\{\hat{\psi}_{1}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\psi}_{\ell}^{n}\}
and
\{\hat{\psi}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\psi}_{\ell}\}
to the
\ell
largest eigenvalues of the operators
\mathcal{R}^{n},\mathcal{R}:X\to X
introduced in (
2.1.9
) and (
2.2.2
), respectively. In this section, we investigate what happens when the discrete snapshots represent an approximation of the continuous trajectory that gets more and more precise as
n\to\infty
. This notion was already introduced at the beginning of Section
2.2
by discretizing the parameter space
\mathscr{D}
into
n
sample points
\{{\bm{\mu}}_{1}^{n},\ldots,{\bm{\mu}}_{n}^{n}\}\subset\mathscr{D}
and setting

\displaystyle y_{j}^{k}=y^{k}({\bm{\mu}}_{j})\quad\text{for }1\leq j\leq n\text{ and }1\leq k\leq{K}.

Just like this discrete trajectory represents the continuous one, we will choose the positive weights
\alpha_{1}^{n},\ldots,\alpha_{n}^{n}
in a way that the sum in the discrete problem (

P
ℓ

n

) provides an approximation for the integral in the continuous problem (

P
ℓ

). Under certain conditions, this will result in a form of convergence of the discrete solution vectors
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
to the continuous ones
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
and of the discrete eigenvalues
\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
to the continuous ones
\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
as
n
tends to
\infty
.

2.3.1
General case in complex Hilbert spaces

In general the spectrum
\sigma(\mathcal{T})
of an operator
\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{L}(X)
will not depend continuously on
\mathcal{T}
. This is an essential difference to the finite dimensional case, where, e.g., the eigenvalues of a quadratic matrix
A=((A_{ij}))\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
depend continuously on the entries
A_{ij}
. However, if the operator contains isolated spectral points, a similar behavior can be proven for those points in the infinite-dimensional case, see Section
A.3
.

We will now apply the general perturbation results from Section
A.3
to the concrete case of the operators
\mathcal{R}^{n}
are
\mathcal{R}
for which we will assume that convergence in the operator norm holds.

Theorem 2.3.1
.

Suppose that the sample points
\{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{n}\}_{j=1}^{n}
and weighting parameters
\{\alpha_{j}^{n}\}_{j=1}^{n}
are chosen in a way such that for every
F\in C(\mathscr{D};X)
, it holds

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}F({\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{n})\to\int_{\mathscr{D}}F({\bm{\mu}})~\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

(2.3.1)

Further, assume that for
1\leq k\leq{K}
, the trajectory is given by
y^{k}\in C(\mathscr{D};X)
and its discrete counterpart is chosen as
y_{j}^{k}:=y^{k}({\bm{\mu}}_{j})
for
1\leq j\leq n
. Let the operators
\mathcal{R}^{n},\mathcal{R}\in\mathscr{L}(X)
defined in (
2.1.2
) and (
2.2.2
) satisfy

{\|\mathcal{R}^{n}-\mathcal{R}\|}_{\mathscr{L}(X)}\to 0\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

(2.3.2)

Let
\{(\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n})\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
and
\{(\hat{\lambda}_{i},\hat{\psi}_{i})\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
denote the sequence of decreasing eigenvalues and of eigenvectors of
\mathcal{R}^{n}
and
\mathcal{R}
satisfying (
2.1.10
) and (
2.2.5
), respectively. Suppose that
\ell\in\{1,...,d\}
is finite. Then we have
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\to\hat{\lambda}_{i}
as
n\to\infty
for every
1\leq i\leq\ell
. Furthermore, if
\ell
satisfies
\hat{\lambda}_{\ell}>\hat{\lambda}_{\ell+1}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d}\hat{\lambda}_{i},

(2.3.3a)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\langle\varphi,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle_{X}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\langle\varphi,\hat{\psi}_{i}\rangle_{X}\hat{\psi}_{i}\quad\text{for }\varphi\in X.

(2.3.3b)

Example 2.3.2
.

We want to illustrate the validity of Theorem
2.3.1
by continuing our general Example
1.3.1
. The control value is set to
u(t)=(0,0)^{\top}
for all
t\in(0,T)
. The parameter domain
\mathscr{D}
coincides with the time axis
(0,T)\subset\mathbb{R}
. We discretize the infinite-dimensional space
V=H^{1}(\Omega)
by the piecewise linear FE method (cf., e.g.,
[
BS08
,
Tho06
]
and Section
3.4
) using
m=1319
ansatz functions. Therefore, the Hilbert space
X
is given by
\mathbb{R}^{m}
with the inner product induced by the stiffness matrix
{\bm{S}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
from Example
2.1.25
approximating the inner product in
V
. In the context of (

P
ℓ

), we have
K=1
,
\omega_{1}^{1}=1
and
y^{1}\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{m})
being the weak solution to the FE-discretized PDE (
1.2.1
) with still-continuous time axis. We ensure the integral approximation (
2.3.1
) by choosing the usual equidistant trapezoidal weights given in (
2.1.4
). The step size
\Delta t
for the time discretization is inversely proportional to
n
and we choose the values

n\in\{10,50,100,200,400,1.000,4000,10000\}=:\mathrm{N}.

(2.3.4)

For the time integration of the FE-discretized PDE (
1.2.1
) we apply an implicit Euler scheme with
n+1
time instances
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{n}=T
. The discretization will be described in detail in Section
3.5
. The discrete solutions are denoted by
\{y^{n}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
for all
n
in (
2.3.4
), and are also the data trajectories for the discrete POD problem (

P
ℓ

n

). Thus having set up both the discrete and the continuous POD problem, we would like to solve both problems and compare the continuous eigenvalues
\hat{\lambda}_{i}
to the discrete eigenvalues
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}
for increasing
n
. However, it was already mentioned that the continuous problem can not be solved numerically, so we use the discrete problem with
n=10000
as an approximation and introduce for the first eight eigenvalues

\mu_{i}^{n}:=\big|\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}-\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{10000}\big|\qquad\text{for }n\in\mathrm{N},~i=1,...,8.

(2.3.5)

Figure 2.3.1
:
Example
2.3.2
. The values
{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{n}
from (
2.3.5
) indicating the convergence of POD eigenvalues
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}
as
n\to\infty
.

The results are displayed in Figure
2.3.1
, where we can observe a clearly monotonous decay with
n
in the “error” terms
\mu_{i}^{n}
, suggesting that these discrete eigenvalues truly approximate the analytical eigenvalues as
n\to\infty
, as it was suggested by Theorem
2.3.1
.
\blacklozenge

Example 2.3.3
.

We can observe a similar asymptotic convergence as in Example
2.3.2
if we fix the time discretization at
n=400
and choose different dimensions
m
of the FE spaces

m\in\{139,203,263,359,494,765,1319,2999\}=:\mathrm{M}.

(2.3.6)

This gives us discrete solutions
\{y_{j}^{m}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset X^{m}
, to each of which we solve the discrete POD problem (

P
ℓ

n

) and get eigenvectors
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{m}
, this time indicated by the dependence on
m
. Figure
2.3.2
shows the decay of the error in these eigenvalues, which is computed, as in (
2.3.5
), by

\nu_{i}^{m}=\big|\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{m}-\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{2999}\big|\qquad\text{for }m\in\mathrm{M},~i=1,...,8.

(2.3.7)

Figure 2.3.2
:
Example
2.3.3
. The values
\nu_{i}^{m}
from (
2.3.7
) indicating the convergence of POD eigenvalues
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{m}
as
m\to\infty
.

A similar decay as for the time-refinement case can be seen in Figure
2.3.2
, indicating that by taking the limit
m\to\infty
, we are approaching the eigenvalues for the time-discretized weak solution to the PDE (
1.2.1
) which is still continuous in the space-dimension. The reason for this lies in the fact that the subspace
X^{m}
is isometrically isomorphic to a subspace
V^{m}
of
V=H^{1}(\Omega)
(spanned by the FE basis vectors) which becomes an increasingly good approximation of it as
m\to\infty
. Taking both error decays as
n,m\to\infty
together suggests that we are approaching the solution to the fully-continuous POD problem; where the time axis is still continuous and
X
is given by
V
, exceedingly well.
\blacklozenge

2.3.2
The discrete POD method revisited

Theorem
2.3.1
relies essentially on the convergence of
\mathcal{R}^{n}
to
\mathcal{R}
in the operator norm; cf. (
2.3.2
). In this section we give a sufficient condition to ensure (
2.3.2
). To simplify the presentation we consider the case
{K}=1
and apply a (one-dimensional) trapezoidal approximation of the integral operator
\mathcal{R}
defined in (
2.2.2
). As in Section
2.2.1
let the parameter domain
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathbb{R}
be given by the interval
\mathscr{D}=[{\bm{\mu}}^{a},{\bm{\mu}}^{b}]
. For chosen
n\in\mathbb{N}
we define a grid on
[{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{a}},{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{b}}]
as follows:

\displaystyle{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{a}}={\bm{\mu}}_{1}<\ldots<{\bm{\mu}}_{n}={\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{b}},\quad\delta{\bm{\mu}}_{j}={\bm{\mu}}_{j}-{\bm{\mu}}_{j-1}\quad\text{for }2\leq j\leq n.

We set
\Delta{\bm{\mu}}=\max_{2\leq j\leq n}\delta{\bm{\mu}}_{j}
and choose the trapezoidal weights

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}^{n}=\frac{\delta{\bm{\mu}}_{2}}{2},\quad\alpha_{j}^{n}=\frac{\delta{\bm{\mu}}_{j}+\delta{\bm{\mu}}_{j-1}}{2}\quad\text{for }2\leq j\leq n-1,\quad\alpha_{n}^{n}=\frac{\delta{\bm{\mu}}_{n}}{2}.

With the above choices of
\{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
and
\{\alpha_{j}^{n}\}_{j=1}^{n}
, we can view
\mathcal{R}^{n}
as a trapezoidal approximation of
\mathcal{R}
.

Proposition 2.3.4
.

Suppose that the snapshot trajectories
y^{k}
belong to
H^{1}(\mathscr{D};X)
for
1\leq k\leq{K}
and we have
\Delta{\bm{\mu}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Then (
2.3.2
) holds true.

Remark 2.3.5
.

1)

Theorem
2.3.1
and Proposition
2.3.4
give an answer to the two questions posed at the beginning of Section
2.2
: The grid points
\{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
and the associated positive weights
\{\alpha_{j}^{n}\}_{j=1}^{n}
should be chosen such that
\mathcal{R}^{n}
is a quadrature approximation of
\mathcal{R}
and
\|\mathcal{R}^{n}-\mathcal{R}\|_{\mathscr{L}(X)}
is small (for reasonable
n
). In Proposition
2.3.4
it was shown that this can for example be obtained if the weights
\{\alpha_{j}^{n}\}_{j=1}^{n}
are chosen as trapezoidal weights and the maximal grid size converges to 0. Clearly, other choices for the weights
\{\alpha_{j}^{n}\}_{j=1}^{n}
are also possible provided (
2.3.2
) is guaranteed. For instance, we can choose the Simpson weights. A different strategy is applied in
[
KV10
,
LV14
]
, where the time instances
\{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
are chosen by an optimization approach.

2)

In a more general case,
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{p}}
could be an arbitrary closed, bounded and connected domain with
\mathfrak{p}>1
. In this case, the simple one-dimensional discretization of
\mathscr{D}
has to be replaced by a more complicated finite difference or FE grid and according weights to approximate the integration over
\mathscr{D}
. The above proof of convergence
\mathcal{R}^{n}\to\mathcal{R}
in the operator norm then has to be adapted for this more general case.
\blacksquare

2.3.3
Perturbation analysis in
\bm{\mathbb{C}^{m}}

Let us assume that
X=\mathbb{C}^{m}
. To simplify the presentation we assume
K=1
and
\omega_{1}^{K}=1
. The snapshots
y_{j}=(\mathrm{y}_{ij})_{1\leq i\leq m}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
,
1\leq j\leq n
, are again supposed to be approximations of
y({\bm{\mu}})=(\mathrm{y}_{i}({\bm{\mu}}))_{1\leq i\leq m}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
at the grid points
{\bm{\mu}}={\bm{\mu}}_{j}\in\mathscr{D}
,
1\leq j\leq n
. Again we set
{\bm{Y}}=[y_{1}\,|\ldots|\,y_{n}]\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
. In Section
2.1.7
we have shown that
\mathcal{R}^{n}={\bm{Y}}{\bm{Y}}^{\mathsf{H}}
holds. Next we derive a matrix representation of the operator
\mathcal{R}
. For
\mathrm{v}=(\mathrm{v}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq m}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
and
1\leq i\leq m
we have

\displaystyle(\mathcal{R}\mathrm{v})_{i}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathscr{D}}{\langle\mathrm{v},y({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\,\mathrm{y}_{i}({\bm{\mu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}=\int_{\mathscr{D}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathrm{y}_{i}({\bm{\mu}})\overline{\mathrm{y}_{j}({\bm{\mu}})}\mathrm{v}_{j}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\Big(\int_{\mathscr{D}}\mathrm{y}_{i}({\bm{\mu}})\overline{\mathrm{y}_{j}({\bm{\mu}})}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\Big)\mathrm{v}_{j}.

Therefore, the matrix
\mathcal{R}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}
is given by the elements

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{ij}=\int_{\mathscr{D}}\mathrm{y}_{i}({\bm{\mu}})\overline{\mathrm{y}_{j}({\bm{\mu}})}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\quad\text{for }1\leq i,j\leq m.

Notice that the matrix
\mathcal{R}
can be interpreted as a correlation matrix. Since both
\mathcal{R}^{n}
and
\mathcal{R}
are Hermitian matrices, we can apply well-known results from perturbation theory to estimate the differences between associated eigenvalues; see, e.g.,
[
Cha11
, Chapter 1]
.

Proposition 2.3.6
.

Suppose that the first
\ell\leq d
eigenvalues of
\mathcal{R}
have algebraic and geometric multiplicity one, i.e.,
\hat{\lambda}_{1}>\hat{\lambda}_{2}>\ldots>\hat{\lambda}_{\ell}
. If
\varepsilon=\|\mathcal{R}-\mathcal{R}^{n}\|_{2}
is small enough, we have

\displaystyle\big|\hat{\lambda}_{i}-\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\big|=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{dist}\,\left(\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n},\mathrm{ker}\big(\mathcal{R}-\hat{\lambda}_{i}{\bm{I}}\big)\right)=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)\quad\text{for }1\leq i\leq\ell

with the minimal distance function

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}\,\left(\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n},\mathrm{ker}\big(\mathcal{R}-\hat{\lambda}_{i}{\bm{I}}\big)\right)=\min\left\{{\|\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}-v\|}_{2}\,\big|\,v\in\mathrm{ker}\big(\mathcal{R}-\hat{\lambda}_{i}{\bm{I}}\big)\right\}

and
{\bm{I}}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}
stands for the identity matrix.

Remark 2.3.7
.

For the more general case that the algebraic and geometric multiplicity of the eigenvalues are different from one, we refer the reader to
[
Wil65
, Chapter 2]
and
[
Kat95
, Chapter 2]
.
\blacksquare

2.4
POD for embedded Hilbert spaces

In this section we discuss a specific choice for the Hilbert space
X
which often occurs in conjunction with reduced-order modelling of evolution problems, cf. Chapter
3
. It turns out that in this case it is possible to derive rate of convergence results which will be very useful in our a-priori error analysis carried out in Sections
3
and
4
. The presented results are essentially based on
[
Sin14
]
. We make the following basic assumption throughout this section.

Assumption 2.4.1
.

Let
H
and
V
be two separable Hilbert spaces with
V\subset H
. We assume that
V
is dense in
H
, i.e., for any
\varphi\in H
there exists a sequence
\{\varphi_{\nu}\}_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}}
in
V
such that
\|\varphi_{\nu}-\varphi\|_{H}\to 0
as
\nu\to\infty
. Moreover,
V
is continuously embedded in
H
.

Remark 2.4.2
.

It follows from Assumption
2.4.1
that there exists a constant
c_{V}>0
satisfying

{\|\varphi\|}_{H}\leq c_{V}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in V.

(2.4.1)

Throughout the book we refer to (
2.4.1
) with the name
embedding inequality
. Identifying the Hilbert space
H
with its dual space
H^{\prime}
, we get the so-called
Gelfand triple

V\hookrightarrow H\simeq H^{\prime}\hookrightarrow V^{\prime}
.
\blacksquare

Example 2.4.3
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a bounded domain.

1)

We have previously defined the Hilbert spaces
H=L^{2}(\Omega)
and
V=H^{1}(\Omega)
in Examples
2.1.7
and
2.1.8
with their usual inner products

\displaystyle{\langle\varphi,\phi\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\varphi({\bm{x}})\phi({\bm{x}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle\text{for }\varphi,\phi\in H,

\displaystyle{\langle\varphi,\phi\rangle}_{V}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\varphi({\bm{x}})\phi({\bm{x}})+\nabla\varphi({\bm{x}})\cdot\nabla\phi({\bm{x}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle\text{for }\varphi,\phi\in V

and the induced norms
\|\cdot\|_{H}=\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle_{H}^{1/2}
and
\|\cdot\|_{V}=\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle_{V}^{1/2}
, respectively. It is well-known that these spaces are separable and that
V
is densely as well as continuously embedded in
H
; see, e.g.,
[
Eva08
]
. In this case the embedding constant is given by
c_{V}=1
.

2)

Another example for Hilbert spaces which form a Gelfand triple is given by
H=L^{2}(\Omega)
and
V=H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
, where the inner product in
V
is defined as

\displaystyle{\langle\varphi,\phi\rangle}_{V}=\int_{\Omega}\nabla\varphi({\bm{x}})\cdot\nabla\phi({\bm{x}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\quad\text{for }\varphi,\phi\in V

with the induced norm
\|\cdot\|_{V}=\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle_{V}^{1/2}
. In this case the embedding
V\hookrightarrow H
can be obtained by the Poincaré inequality and the embedding constant
c_{V}
is hence given by the Poincaré constant; see
[
Eva08
, p. 265]
, for instance.
\blacklozenge

2.4.1
Continuous POD version

Now we study (

P
ℓ

) for two different choices of the separable Hilbert space
X
, namely for
X=H
and
X=V
. Suppose that we are given snapshots
y^{k}\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D};V)
,
1\leq k\leq{K}
. Then the snapshot space
\mathscr{V}
is a subset of
V
, and thus in particular of
H
. We consider

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\min\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\Big\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y^{k}({\bm{\mu}}),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{H}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\\
&\hskip 1.42262pt\text{s.t. }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset H\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{H}=\delta_{ij},~1\leq i,j\leq\ell,\end{aligned}\right.

(
\mathbf{P}^{\ell}_{H}
)

and

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\min\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\Big\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y^{k}({\bm{\mu}}),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{V}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\\
&\hskip 1.42262pt\text{s.t. }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset V\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{V}=\delta_{ij},~1\leq i,j\leq\ell.\end{aligned}\right.

(
\mathbf{P}^{\ell}_{V}
)

Example 2.4.4
.

In Example
2.1.7
, we have already considered the parabolic example with the choices
X=V
and
X=H
. For convenience, we consider only the continuous problem (

P
ℓ

) in our notation. Of course, all numerical results are generated by fully discrete methods and can only serve as an illustration of this infinite-dimensional case. Figure
2.1.5
already shows the difference between the eigenvalues
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{V}
and
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{H}
. It can be seen the
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{V}
is always larger than
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{H}
. The reason for this lies in the fact that we have

\displaystyle{\|\psi\|}_{V}^{2}={\|\psi\|}_{H}^{2}+\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\psi({\bm{x}})|^{2}_{2}~\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\geq{\|\psi\|}_{H}^{2}\qquad\text{for all }\psi\in V.

This means that the
V
-basis has to approximate both the values of
y^{k}(t,\cdot)
and their gradients
\nabla y^{k}(t,\cdot)
, while the
H
-basis only has to do the former, which is clearly the easier task. By the growing discrepancy between
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{V}
and
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{H}
as
i
increases, we can observe that this difference in the approximation demand increases as well. In addition, note that
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
“only” has to be
H
-orthonormal whereas
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
is supposed to be
V
-orthonormal. In terms of functions, this especially implies that each
\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V}
is limited in terms of sharp edges since high values of the gradient have to be avoided due to
\|\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V}\|_{V}=1
.

Figure 2.4.1
:
Example
2.4.4
. First six POD basis vectors
\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H}
.

In Figure
2.4.1
, we see the first six POD vectors for the choice
X=H
, which can be compared to Figure
2.1.6
with the choice
X=V
. We can immediately validate the above observation since every basis vector
\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H}
has a much wider range than its
V
-counterpart
\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V}
.

In conclusion, the choice
X=H
makes the problem much easier since the basis is less limited in terms of gradient jumps and the demanded approximation quality is lower than for
X=V
. In practise, the users must make their choice based on the problem at hand. For example, if it is essential to include gradient information in the basis, the
V
-approach is imperative. We can also consider mixed cases, where, e.g., we require a
V
-orthonormal POD basis, but look for only
H
-approximation of the trajectories, which leads to a problem of the type

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\min\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\int_{0}^{T}\bigg\|y^{k}(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y^{k}(t),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{H}\psi_{i}\bigg\|_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 2.84526pt\text{s.t. }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset V\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{V}=\delta_{ij}\quad\text{for }i,j=1,...,\ell.\end{aligned}\right.

Now, the first-order conditions are given as a generalized eigenvalue problem.
\blacklozenge

Optimal solutions to (

P
ℓ

H

) and (

P
ℓ

V

)

The solutions to (

P
ℓ

H

) and (

P
ℓ

V

) are given by Theorem
2.2.7
. Therefore, let us define the two linear operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{H}\psi

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}{\langle\psi,y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{H}\,y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}

\displaystyle\text{for }\psi\in H,

(2.4.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{V}\psi

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}{\langle\psi,y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{V}\,y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}

\displaystyle\text{for }\psi\in V.

We set

\displaystyle d^{H}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{rank}\big(\mathrm{ran}(\mathcal{R}_{H})\big)&&\text{if }\mathrm{rank}\big(\mathrm{ran}(\mathcal{R}_{H})\big)\text{ is finite},\\
&\infty&&\text{otherwise}\end{aligned}\right.

and

\displaystyle d^{V}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{rank}\big(\mathrm{ran}(\mathcal{R}_{V})\big)&&\text{if }\mathrm{rank}\big(\mathrm{ran}(\mathcal{R}_{V})\big)\text{ is finite},\\
&\infty&&\text{otherwise}.\end{aligned}\right.

Since
y^{k}\in L^{2}(0,T;V)
holds, we have
\mathcal{R}_{H}\psi\in V
for all
\psi\in H
and
\mathcal{R}_{V}\psi\in V
for all
\psi\in V
. Nevertheless,
\mathcal{R}_{H}
is considered by definition as an operator mapping from
H
to
H
. By Lemma
2.2.5
both
\mathcal{R}_{H}
and
\mathcal{R}_{V}
are compact, self-adjoint and non-negative. Thus, it follows from Theorem
2.2.7
that there exist non-negative eigenvalues
\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{H}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
and associated orthonormal eigenfunctions
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\subset H
satisfying

\mathcal{R}_{H}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{H}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H},\hskip 5.69054pt\left\{\begin{aligned} &\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{H}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{d^{H}}^{H}>\hat{\lambda}_{d^{H}+1}^{H}=\ldots=0&&\text{if }d^{H}<\infty,\\
&\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{H}\geq\hat{\lambda}_{2}^{H}\geq\ldots\text{ and }\lim_{i\to\infty}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{H}=0&&\text{if }d^{H}=\infty.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.4.3)

with
d^{H}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. For every
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d^{H}\}
the first
\ell
eigenfunctions
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
solve (

P
ℓ

H

). When the POD basis
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell
is computed, we set

\displaystyle H^{\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\{\hat{\psi}_{1}^{H},\ldots,\hat{\psi}_{\ell}^{H}\}.

Analogously, Theorem
2.2.7
implies the existence of non-negative eigenvalues
\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{V}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
and associated orthonormal eigenfunctions
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\subset V
satisfying

\mathcal{R}_{V}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{V}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V},\hskip 5.69054pt\left\{\begin{aligned} &\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{V}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{d^{V}}^{V}>\hat{\lambda}_{d^{V}+1}^{V}=\ldots=0&&\text{if }d^{V}<\infty,\\
&\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{V}\geq\hat{\lambda}_{2}^{V}\geq\ldots\text{ and }\lim_{i\to\infty}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{V}=0&&\text{if }d^{V}=\infty.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.4.4)

with
d^{V}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. The first
\ell
eigenfunctions
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
solve (

P
ℓ

V

) for every
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d^{V}\}
. We define the
\ell
-dimensional subspace

\displaystyle V^{\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\{\hat{\psi}_{1}^{V},\ldots,\hat{\psi}_{\ell}^{V}\}\subset V.

Moreover, we infer from (
2.2.6
) the approximation formulas

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\Big\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y^{k}({\bm{\mu}}),\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H}\rangle}_{H}\,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H}\Big\|_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}=\sum_{i>\ell}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{H},

(2.4.5a)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\Big\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y^{k}({\bm{\mu}}),\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V}\rangle}_{V}\,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V}\Big\|_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}=\sum_{i>\ell}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{V}.

(2.4.5b)

Remark 2.4.5
.

As
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
and
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
are orthonormal in
H
and
V
, respectively, we derive that
((\langle\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H},\hat{\psi}_{j}^{H}\rangle_{H}))={\bm{I}}
and
((\langle\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V},\hat{\psi}_{j}^{V}\rangle_{V}))={\bm{I}}
, respectively. Additionally, as both
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
and
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
are linearly independent in
V
and
H
, respectively, it is easy to see that the matrices
((\langle\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H},\hat{\psi}_{j}^{H}\rangle_{V}))
and
((\langle\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V},\hat{\psi}_{j}^{V}\rangle_{H}))
are positive definite, respectively.
\blacksquare

The relationship between the eigenvalues
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{H}
and
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{V}
is investigated in the next lemma, which is taken from
[
Sin14
, Lemma 3.1]
. We also refer the reader to
[
GV17
, Lemma 3.4]
.

Lemma 2.4.6
.

Let Assumption
2.4.1
be satisfied. Suppose that the snapshots fulfill
y^{k}\in L^{2}(0,T;V)
for
k=1,\ldots,{K}
. Then:

1)

For all
i\in\{1,\ldots,d_{H}\}
we have
\hat{\psi}^{H}_{i}\in V
.

2)

\lambda_{i}^{V}=0
for all
i>d
with some
d\in\mathbb{N}
if and only if
\lambda_{i}^{H}=0
for all
i>d
, i.e., we have
d^{H}=d^{V}
provided the rank of
\mathcal{R}_{V}
or of
\mathcal{R}_{H}
is finite.

3)

\lambda_{i}^{V}>0
for all
i\in\mathbb{I}
if and only if
\lambda_{i}^{H}>0
for all
i\in\mathbb{I}
.

Remark 2.4.7
.

We infer from Lemma
2.4.6
-1) it follows that the space
H^{\ell}
is an
\ell
-dimensional subspace of
V
. Furthermore, Lemma
2.4.6
-1) and 2) imply that only one of the following cases can occur:

1)

There exists a
d\in\mathbb{N}
such that
d=d_{H}=d_{V}
and
\lambda_{i}^{H}=\lambda_{i}^{V}=0
for
i>d
.

2)

For all
i\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\lambda_{i}^{H}>0
as well as
\lambda_{i}^{V}>0
.
\blacksquare

POD projection operators

Let us introduce the following four projection operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}:H\to H^{\ell},

\displaystyle v^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}\varphi

\displaystyle\text{solves }\min_{w^{\ell}\in H^{\ell}}{\|\varphi-w^{\ell}\|}_{H}\text{ for }\varphi\in H,

(2.4.6a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}:V\to H^{\ell},

\displaystyle v^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}\varphi

\displaystyle\text{solves }\min_{w^{\ell}\in H^{\ell}}{\|\varphi-w^{\ell}\|}_{V}\text{ for }\varphi\in V,

(2.4.6b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}:H\to V^{\ell},

\displaystyle u^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}\varphi

\displaystyle\text{solves }\min_{w^{\ell}\in V^{\ell}}{\|\varphi-w^{\ell}\|}_{H}\text{ for }\varphi\in H,

(2.4.6c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{V}:V\to V^{\ell},

\displaystyle u^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{V}\varphi

\displaystyle\text{solves }\min_{w^{\ell}\in V^{\ell}}{\|\varphi-w^{\ell}\|}_{V}\text{ for }\varphi\in V.

(2.4.6d)
