These operators will play an essential role in our error analysis carried out in Sections
3
and
4
.

In the following lemma we state how the projection operators can be computed. Furthermore, we list essential properties of these operators. For the proof we refer the reader to Section
2.5.4
.

Proposition 2.4.8
.

Let Assumption
2.4.1
hold. Suppose that the snapshots fulfill
y^{k}\in L^{2}(0,T;V)
for
k=1,\ldots,{K}
. The four projections in (
2.4.6
) are given as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}\varphi

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle\varphi,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H}\rangle}_{H}\,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H}

\displaystyle\text{for }\varphi\in H,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}\varphi

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{v}_{i}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H}

\displaystyle\text{for }\varphi\in V,

(2.4.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{V}\varphi

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle\varphi,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V}\rangle}_{V}\,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V}

\displaystyle\text{for }\varphi\in V,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}\varphi

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{u}_{i}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V}

\displaystyle\text{for }\varphi\in H,

where the vectors
\mathrm{v}=(\mathrm{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathrm{v}_{\ell})^{\top},\,\mathrm{u}=(\mathrm{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathrm{u}_{\ell})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
solve the following linear systems

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}{\langle\hat{\psi}_{j}^{H},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H}\rangle}_{V}\,\mathrm{v}_{j}={\langle\varphi,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H}\rangle}_{V}

\displaystyle\text{for }1\leq i\leq\ell,

(2.4.8a)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}{\langle\hat{\psi}_{j}^{V},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V}\rangle}_{H}\,\mathrm{u}_{j}={\langle\varphi,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V}\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle\text{for }1\leq i\leq\ell,

(2.4.8b)

respectively. All four projections are linear and bounded. Furthermore,
\mathcal{P}_{H}^{\ell},\,\mathcal{Q}_{H}^{\ell}
are orthogonal in
H
and
\mathcal{P}_{V}^{\ell},\,\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{\ell}
are orthogonal in
V
.

Remark 2.4.9
.

Let Assumption
2.4.1
hold. Then we infer from Proposition
2.4.8
that the approximation formulas (
2.4.5
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\Big\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})-\mathcal{P}_{H}^{\ell}y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\Big\|_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}=\sum_{i>\ell}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{H},

(2.4.9a)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\Big\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})-\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{\ell}y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\Big\|_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}=\sum_{i>\ell}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{V}.

(2.4.9b)

Hence, we do not only have
\|y^{k}-\mathcal{P}_{H}^{\ell}y^{k}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathscr{D};H)}\to 0
and
\|y^{k}-\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{\ell}y^{k}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathscr{D};V)}\to 0
as
\ell\to\infty
for
k=1,\ldots,K
, but the rate of convergence is given as
\sum_{i>\ell}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{H}
and
\sum_{i>\ell}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{V}
, respectively. Especially, the decay rate of the eigenvalues
\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{H}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
or
\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{V}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
is essential for the speed of convergence.
\blacksquare

Singular value decomposition

In Remark
2.2.9
we have introduced the
singular value decomposition
of the operator
\mathcal{Y}
defined by (
2.2.3
). By
\mathcal{Y}_{H}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{V}
we denote the operator
\mathcal{Y}
considered as a mapping in
\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(\mathscr{D},\mathbb{C}^{K}),H)
and
\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(\mathscr{D},\mathbb{C}^{K}),V)
, respectively. Then the associated adjoints
\mathcal{Y}_{H}^{\star}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{V}^{\star}
satisfy

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{Y}^{\star}_{H}\psi)({\bm{\mu}})

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\langle\psi,y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{H}\\
\vdots\\
{\langle\psi,y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{H}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle\text{for }\psi\in H\text{ and }{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathscr{D},

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{Y}^{\star}_{V}\psi)({\bm{\mu}})

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\langle\psi,y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{V}\\
\vdots\\
{\langle\psi,y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{V}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle\text{for }\psi\in V\text{ and }{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathscr{D}.

The operators
\mathcal{K}_{H}=\mathcal{Y}^{H,\star}\mathcal{Y}^{H},\mathcal{K}_{V}=\mathcal{Y}^{V,\star}\mathcal{Y}^{V}:L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K})\to L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K})
are given by

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{K}_{H}\phi\big)({\bm{\mu}})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}{\langle y^{k}({\bm{\nu}}),y^{1}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{H}\phi^{k}({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}\\
\vdots\\
\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}{\langle y^{k}({\bm{\nu}}),y^{K}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{H}\phi^{k}({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}\end{array}\right)\quad\text{for }\phi\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K}),

and

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{K}_{V}\phi\big)({\bm{\mu}})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}{\langle y^{k}({\bm{\nu}}),y^{1}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{V}\phi^{k}({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}\\
\vdots\\
\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}{\langle y^{k}({\bm{\nu}}),y^{K}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{V}\phi^{k}({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}\end{array}\right)\quad\text{for }\phi\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K}),

respectively. Due to singular value decomposition, there exists a sequence
\{\hat{\phi}_{i}^{H}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\subset L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K})
satisfying

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{H}\hat{\phi}_{i}^{H}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{H}\hat{\phi}_{i}^{H},\quad\mathcal{R}_{H}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{H}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H},\quad\mathcal{Y}_{H}\hat{\phi}_{i}^{H}=\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{H}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H},\quad\mathcal{Y}^{\star}_{H}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H}=\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{H}\hat{\phi}_{i}^{H}

for
i\in\mathbb{I}
and
\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{H}=(\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{H})^{1/2}
. Analogously, there is a sequence
\{\hat{\phi}_{i}^{V}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\subset L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K})
satisfying

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{V}\hat{\phi}_{i}^{V}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{V}\hat{\phi}_{i}^{V},\quad\mathcal{R}_{V}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{V}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H},\quad\mathcal{Y}_{V}\hat{\phi}_{i}^{V}=\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{V}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V},\quad\mathcal{Y}^{\star}_{V}\psi_{i}^{V}=\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{V}\hat{\phi}_{i}^{V}

for
i\in\mathbb{I}
and
\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{V}=(\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{V})^{1/2}
.

A-priori error analysis

In the next proposition we summarize convergence properties of the projection operators introduced in (
2.4.6
). For a proof we refer the reader to
[
Sin14
, Proposition 5.5]
.

Proposition 2.4.10
.

Suppose that Assumption
2.4.1
holds and the snapshots fulfill
y^{k}\in L^{2}(0,T;V)
for
k=1,\ldots,{K}
. Then
\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\|\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{\ell}\varphi-\varphi\|_{V}=0
for every
\varphi\in V
. If
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{H}>0
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

1)

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\|\mathcal{P}_{H}^{\ell}\varphi-\varphi\|_{H}=0
for every
\varphi\in\mathrm{ran}\,(\mathcal{Y}_{H})
,

2)

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\|\mathcal{P}_{V}^{\ell}\varphi-\varphi\|_{V}=0
for every
\varphi\in V
.

Convergence properties in the
V
-topology for the snapshots
\{y^{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}\subset L^{2}(\mathscr{D},V)
are presented in the following theorem. The proof is given in
[
Sin14
, Theorems 5.2 and 5.3]
.

Theorem 2.4.11
.

Suppose that Assumption
2.4.1
and
y^{k}\in L^{2}(0,T;V)
for
k=1,\ldots,K
hold.

1)

If
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{H}>0
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\lim_{\ell\to\infty}{\|\mathcal{P}_{H}^{\ell}y^{k}-y^{k}\|}_{L^{2}(\mathscr{D};V)}=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}{\|\mathcal{P}_{V}^{\ell}y^{k}-y^{k}\|}_{L^{2}(\mathscr{D};V)}=0\quad\text{for }1\leq k\leq K.

2)

If
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{V}>0
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
\|\mathcal{Q}_{H}^{\ell}\|_{\mathscr{L}(V)}
is bounded independently of
\ell
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\ell\to\infty}{\|\mathcal{Q}_{H}^{\ell}y^{k}-y^{k}\|}_{L^{2}(\mathscr{D};V)}=0\quad\text{for }1\leq k\leq K.

3)

It holds

\displaystyle\lim_{\ell\to\infty}{\|\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{\ell}y^{k}-y^{k}\|}_{L^{2}(\mathscr{D};V)}=0\quad\text{for }1\leq k\leq K.

Remark 2.4.12
.

The assumption that
\|\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}\|_{\mathscr{L}(V)}
is bounded independently of
\ell
was also supposed in
[
CGSM12
]
.
\blacksquare

Recall the error formulas (
2.4.5
). Moreover, we have further error formulas for the
V
-topology, which will play an essential role in the error analysis carried out in Chapters
3
and
4
. Proofs can be found in
[
Sin14
, Theorems 5.2, 5.3 and 5.4]
.

Theorem 2.4.13
.

Suppose that Assumption
2.4.1
and
y^{k}\in L^{2}(0,T;V)
for
k=1,\ldots,K
hold. For every
\ell\geq 1
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\big\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\big\|_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i>\ell}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{H}<\infty,

(2.4.10a)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\big\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\big\|_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i>\ell}^{d}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{H}\,{\|\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H}\|}_{V}^{2}<\infty,

(2.4.10b)

\displaystyle\hskip-2.84526pt\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\big\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\big\|_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i>\ell}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{H}\big\|\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H}-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{H}\big\|_{V}^{2}<\infty,

(2.4.10c)

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\big\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})-\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\big\|_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{V}\,{\|\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V}-\mathcal{Q}_{H}^{\ell}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{V}\|}_{V}^{2}<\infty,

(2.4.10d)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\big\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})-\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{V}y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\big\|_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i>\ell}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{V}<\infty.

(2.4.10e)

Remark 2.4.14
.

Instead of only having a decay rate of the eigenvalues as in the formulas (
2.4.10a
) and (
2.4.10e
), there are additional damping factors in the a-priori error formulas (
2.4.10b
), (
2.4.10c
) and (
2.4.10d
).
\blacksquare

2.4.2
Discrete POD version

The presented results in Section
2.4.1
can also be applied for the discrete variant of the POD method introduced in Section
2.1
. Again the separable Hilbert space
X
stands either for
H
or
V
. We suppose that the
snapshots

y_{j}^{k}\in V
are given for
1\leq j\leq n
and
1\leq k\leq{K}
. Then the
snapshot space
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathscr{V}^{n}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{y_{j}^{k}\,\big|\,1\leq j\leq n\text{ and }1\leq k\leq{K}\big\}\subset V\hookrightarrow H.

Of course, now the snapshot set
\mathscr{V}^{n}
has finite dimension. Due to Remark
2.4.7
-1) we have
d^{n}=\dim\mathscr{V}^{n}\leq nK
for
X=H
and
X=V
. We consider the minimization problems

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\min\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\Big\|y^{k}_{j}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y^{k}_{j},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{H}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{H}^{2}\\
&\hskip 1.42262pt\text{s.t. }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset H\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{H}=\delta_{ij},~1\leq i,j\leq\ell,\end{aligned}\right.

(
\mathbf{P}^{n\ell}_{H}
)

and

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\min\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\Big\|y^{k}_{j}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y^{k}_{j},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{V}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{V}^{2}\\
&\hskip 1.42262pt\text{s.t. }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset V\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{V}=\delta_{ij},~1\leq i,j\leq\ell.\end{aligned}\right.

(
\mathbf{P}^{n\ell}_{V}
)

Optimal solutions to (

P

⁢
n
ℓ

H

) and (

P

⁢
n
ℓ

V

)

The solutions to (

P

⁢
n
ℓ

H

) and (

P

⁢
n
ℓ

V

) are given by Theorem
2.1.14
. Therefore, let us define the two linear operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{H}^{n}\psi=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\langle y^{k}_{j},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{H}y_{j}^{k}\text{ for }\psi\in H,\quad\mathcal{R}_{V}^{n}\psi=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\langle y^{k}_{j},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{V}y_{j}^{k}\text{ for }\psi\in V.

Clearly,
\mathcal{R}_{H}^{n}
and
\mathcal{R}_{V}^{n}
are finite rank operators having the same rank
d^{n}\leq nK
. Moreover, it follows from Lemma
2.1.10
that both
\mathcal{R}_{H}^{n}
and
\mathcal{R}_{V}^{n}
are compact, self-adjoint and non-negative. Let
\{(\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{nH},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nH})\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
and
\{(\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{nV},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nV})\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
denote the eigenvalue-eigenfunction pairs satisfying

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{H}^{n}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nH}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{nH}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nH},\quad\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{nH}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{d^{n}}^{nH}>\hat{\lambda}_{d^{n}+1}^{nH}=\ldots=0

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{V}^{n}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nV}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{nV},n\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nV},\quad\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{nV}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{d^{n}}^{nV}>\hat{\lambda}_{d^{n}+1}^{nV}=\ldots=0.

For any
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d^{n}\}
the first
\ell
eigenfunctions
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nH}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
solve (

P

⁢
n
ℓ

H

). Further,
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nV}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
is a solution to (

P

⁢
n
ℓ

V

) for any
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d^{n}\}
. Since
\mathcal{R}_{H}^{n}
and
\mathcal{R}_{V}^{n}
are finite rank operators, Lemma
2.4.6
implies the next result.

Lemma 2.4.15
.

Suppose that the snapshots
y^{k}_{j}
belong to
V
for all
1\leq j\leq n
and
1\leq k\leq{K}
. Then
\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nH}\in V
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,d^{n}\}
.

POD projection operators

We introduce the two subspaces

\displaystyle H^{n\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\hat{\psi}_{1}^{nH},\ldots,\hat{\psi}_{\ell}^{nH}\big\}\subset V\quad\text{and}\quad V^{n\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\hat{\psi}_{1}^{nV},\ldots,\hat{\psi}_{\ell}^{nV}\big\}\subset V.

As in (
2.4.6
) we define the projection operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}_{H}:H\to H^{n,\ell},

\displaystyle v^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}_{H}\varphi

\displaystyle\text{solves }\min_{w^{\ell}\in H^{n\ell}}{\|\varphi-w^{\ell}\|}_{H}\text{ for }\varphi\in H,

(2.4.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}_{V}:V\to H^{n,\ell},

\displaystyle u^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}_{V}\varphi

\displaystyle\text{ solves }\min_{w^{\ell}\in H^{n\ell}}{\|\varphi-w^{\ell}\|}_{V}\text{ for }\varphi\in V,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{n\ell}_{H}:H\to V^{n,\ell},

\displaystyle v^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{n\ell}_{H}\varphi

\displaystyle\text{solves }\min_{w^{\ell}\in V^{n\ell}}{\|\varphi-w^{\ell}\|}_{H}\text{ for }\varphi\in H,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{n\ell}_{V}:V\to V^{n,\ell},

\displaystyle u^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{n\ell}_{V}\varphi

\displaystyle\text{solves }\min_{w^{\ell}\in V^{n\ell}}{\|\varphi-w^{\ell}\|}_{V}\text{ for }\varphi\in V.

The next result can be shown similarly as Proposotion
2.4.8
.

Proposition 2.4.16
.

Let Assumption
2.4.1
be satisfied. Suppose that the snapshots fulfill
y^{k}_{j}\in V
for
k=1,\ldots,{K}
,
1\leq j\leq n
. The four projections in (
2.4.11
) are given as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}_{H}\varphi

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle\varphi,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nH}\rangle}_{H}\,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nH}

\displaystyle\text{for }\varphi\in H,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}_{V}\varphi

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{v}_{i}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nH}

\displaystyle\text{for }\varphi\in V,

(2.4.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{n\ell}_{V}\varphi

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle\varphi,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nV}\rangle}_{V}\,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nV}

\displaystyle\text{for }\varphi\in V,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{n\ell}_{H}\varphi

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{u}_{i}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nV}

\displaystyle\text{for }\varphi\in H,

where the vectors
\mathrm{v}=(\mathrm{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathrm{v}_{\ell})^{\top},\,\mathrm{u}=(\mathrm{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathrm{u}_{\ell})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
solve the following linear systems

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}{\langle\hat{\psi}_{j}^{nH},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nH}\rangle}_{V}\,\mathrm{v}_{j}={\langle\varphi,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nH}\rangle}_{V}

\displaystyle\text{for }1\leq i\leq\ell,

(2.4.13a)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}{\langle\hat{\psi}_{j}^{nV},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nV}\rangle}_{H}\,\mathrm{u}_{j}={\langle\varphi,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nV}\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle\text{for }1\leq i\leq\ell,

(2.4.13b)

respectively. All four projections are linear and bounded. Furthermore,
\mathcal{P}_{H}^{n\ell},\,\mathcal{Q}_{H}^{n\ell}
are orthogonal in
H
and
\mathcal{P}_{V}^{n\ell},\,\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{n\ell}
are orthogonal in
V
.

A-priori error analysis

We also transfer Theorem
2.4.13
to the discrete case.

Theorem 2.4.17
.

Suppose that Assumption
2.4.1
and
y^{k}_{j}\in V
for
k=1,\ldots,{K}
,
1\leq j\leq n
, hold. We have for all
1\leq\ell\leq d^{n}

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\big\|y^{k}_{j}-\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}_{H}y^{k}_{j}\big\|_{V}^{2}=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{nH}\,{\|\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nH}\|}_{V}^{2}

(2.4.14a)

For all
1\leq\ell\leq d^{n}
it follows that

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\big\|y^{k}_{j}-\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}_{V}y^{k}_{j}\big\|_{H}^{2}=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{nH}\big\|\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nH}-\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}_{V}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nH}\big\|_{V}^{2}.

(2.4.14b)

We further have

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\big\|y^{k}_{j}-\mathcal{Q}^{n\ell}_{H}y^{k}_{j}\big\|_{V}^{2}=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{nV}\,{\|\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nV}-\mathcal{Q}_{H}^{n\ell}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nV}\|}_{V}^{2}

(2.4.14c)

for all
1\leq\ell\leq d^{n}
. Finally, we have for all
1\leq\ell\leq d^{n}

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\big\|y^{k}_{j}-\mathcal{Q}^{n\ell}_{V}y^{k}_{j}\big\|_{V}^{2}=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{nV}.

(2.4.14d)

Example 2.4.18
.

We continue with Example
2.1.7
to illustrate the validity of the equalities in (
2.4.14
). For this purpose, we set
d_{\mathsf{max}}=50
and compute for
\ell=1,\ldots,d_{\mathsf{max}}
the values

\displaystyle\Delta_{V}(\mathcal{P}_{H}^{n\ell})

\displaystyle=\Big|\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\big\|y^{k}_{j}-\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}_{H}y^{k}_{j}\big\|_{V}^{2}-\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d_{\mathsf{max}}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{nH}\,{\|\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nH}\|}_{V}^{2}\Big|,

\displaystyle\Delta_{H}(\mathcal{P}_{V}^{n\ell})

\displaystyle=\Big|\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\big\|y^{k}_{j}-\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}_{V}y^{k}_{j}\big\|_{H}^{2}-\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d_{\mathsf{max}}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{nH}\big\|\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nH}-\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}_{V}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nH}\big\|_{V}^{2}\Big|,

\displaystyle\Delta_{V}(\mathcal{Q}^{n\ell}_{H})

\displaystyle=\Big|\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\big\|y^{k}_{j}-\mathcal{Q}^{n\ell}_{H}y^{k}_{j}\big\|_{V}^{2}-\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d_{\mathsf{max}}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{nV}\,{\|\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nV}-\mathcal{Q}_{H}^{n\ell}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{nV}\|}_{V}^{2}\Big|,

\displaystyle\Delta_{V}(\mathcal{Q}^{n\ell}_{V})

\displaystyle=\Big|\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\big\|y^{k}_{j}-\mathcal{Q}^{n\ell}_{V}y^{k}_{j}\big\|_{V}^{2}=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d_{\mathsf{max}}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{nV}\Big|.

According to Proposition
2.4.17
, all these terms should vanish for
{d_{\mathsf{max}}}=d^{n}
.
\blacklozenge

2.5
Proofs of Section
2

2.5.1
Proofs of Section
2.1

Proof of Lemma
2.1.10
.
Since the inner product
\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle_{X}
is linear in the first argument, it follows that
\mathcal{R}^{n}
is a linear operator. From

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{R}^{n}\psi\|}_{X}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\big|{\langle\psi,y_{j}^{k}\rangle}_{X}\big|\,{\|y_{j}^{k}\|}_{X}\quad\text{for }\psi\in X

and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (see Lemma
A.2.40
) we conclude that
\mathcal{R}^{n}
is bounded, i.e.,
\mathcal{R}^{n}\in\mathscr{L}(X)
holds true. Since
\mathcal{R}^{n}\psi\in\mathscr{V}^{n}
holds for all
\psi\in X
, the range of
\mathcal{R}^{n}
is finite dimensional. Thus,
\mathcal{R}^{n}
is a finite rank operator which is compact; see
[
RS80
, p. 199]
. Next we show that
\mathcal{R}^{n}
is self-adjoint. For that purpose we choose arbitrary elements
\psi,\tilde{\psi}\in X
and consider

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{R}^{n}\psi,\tilde{\psi}\rangle}_{X}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\langle\psi,y_{j}^{k}\rangle}_{X}\,{\langle y_{j}^{k},\tilde{\psi}\rangle}_{X}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}{\langle\psi,\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\overline{\langle y_{j}^{k},\tilde{\psi}\rangle}_{X}y_{j}^{k}\rangle}_{X}

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle\psi,\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\langle\tilde{\psi},y_{j}^{k}\rangle}_{X}y_{j}^{k}\bigg\rangle_{X}={\langle\psi,\mathcal{R}^{n}\tilde{\psi}\rangle}_{X},

where we have used that the weights
\alpha_{j}^{n}
are real-valued for
1\leq j\leq n
. Thus,
\mathcal{R}^{n}
is self-adjoint. For any
\psi\in X
we derive

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{R}^{n}\psi,\psi\rangle}_{X}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\langle\psi,y_{j}^{k}\rangle}_{X}\,{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi\rangle}_{X}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\big|{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}\geq 0.

(2.5.1)

Hence,
\mathcal{R}^{n}
is also non-negative.
\Box

Proof of Lemma
2.1.12
.

1)

We derive (
2.1.11
) directly from (
2.5.1
), (
2.1.10
) and
\|\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\|_{X}=1
. Since
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}=0
holds for
i>d^{n}
, we find (
2.1.12
).

2)

Since
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
is a complete orthonormal basis and
\|y_{j}^{k}\|_{X}<\infty
holds for
1\leq k\leq{K}
,
1\leq j\leq n
, we have

{\|y_{j}^{k}\|}_{X}^{2}=\bigg\langle\sum_{i\in\mathbb{I}}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i},\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{I}}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{\nu}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{\nu}\bigg\rangle_{X}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\big|{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}

(2.5.2)

for
1\leq k\leq{K}
and
1\leq j\leq n
. Thus, we derive from (
2.5.2
), (
2.1.11
) and (
2.1.12
) that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|y_{j}^{k}\|}_{X}^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\big|{\langle y_{j}^{k},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\big|{\langle y_{j}^{k},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}.

3)

Note that (
2.1.10
) implies that
\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\in\mathscr{V}^{n}
for
i\leq d^{n}
and from (
2.1.12
) we can conclude that
\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}
,
i>d^{n}
, belongs to the orthogonal complement
(\mathscr{V}^{n})^{\perp}
.

4)

By (
2.5.2
) the (probably infinite) sum
\sum_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}
is bounded. Notice that

\displaystyle\Big\|y_{j}^{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{X}^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle y_{j}^{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i},y_{j}^{k}-\sum_{l=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{l}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{l}\Big\rangle_{X}

(2.5.3)

\displaystyle={\|y_{j}^{k}\|}_{X}^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\left({\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}{\langle\psi_{i},y_{j}^{k}\rangle}_{X}+\overline{{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{i}\rangle}}_{X}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\sum_{l=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\overline{{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{l}\rangle}}_{X}{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{l}\rangle}_{X}

\displaystyle={\|y_{j}^{k}\|}_{X}^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\big|{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}

holds for any set
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset X
satisfying
\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{l}\rangle_{X}=\delta_{il}
for
1\leq i,l\leq\ell
. Combining (
2.1.13
) and (
2.5.3
), the objective of (

P
ℓ

n

) can be written as stated in (
2.1.14
).
\Box

Proof of Theoren
2.1.14
.
First, let
\{\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}\}\subset X
be an arbitrary orthonormal set in
X
. In terms of the orthonormal basis
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
we get

\displaystyle\psi_{i}=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{I}}\langle\psi_{i},\hat{\psi}_{\mu}^{n}\rangle_{X}\hat{\psi}_{\mu}^{n}\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,\ell.

Inserting these identities into the objective function of (

^
P

ℓ

n

) yields

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}):

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\left|{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\sum_{\mu,\nu\in\mathbb{I}}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\hat{\psi}_{\mu}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\overline{{\langle y_{j}^{k},\hat{\psi}_{\nu}^{n}\rangle}_{X}}{\langle\psi_{i},\hat{\psi}_{\nu}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\overline{{\langle\psi_{i},\hat{\psi}_{\mu}^{n}\rangle}_{X}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu,\nu\in\mathbb{I}}\bigg\langle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\langle\hat{\psi}_{\nu}^{n},y_{j}^{k}\rangle}_{X}y_{j}^{k},\hat{\psi}_{\mu}^{n}\bigg\rangle_{X}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle\psi_{i},\hat{\psi}_{\nu}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\overline{{\langle\psi_{i},\hat{\psi}_{\mu}^{n}\rangle}_{X}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu,\nu\in\mathbb{I}}{\langle\mathcal{R}^{n}\hat{\psi}_{\nu}^{n},\hat{\psi}_{\mu}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle\psi_{i},\hat{\psi}_{\nu}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\overline{{\langle\psi_{i},\hat{\psi}_{\mu}^{n}\rangle}_{X}}.

We use the fact that the vector family
\{\hat{\psi}_{\nu}^{n}\}_{\nu\in\mathbb{I}}
is a series of orthonormal eigenvectors of
\mathcal{R}^{n}
to the eigenvalues
\{\hat{\lambda}^{n}_{\nu}\}_{\nu\in\mathbb{I}}
and further deduce

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathscr{F}(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell})&=\sum_{\mu,\nu\in\mathbb{I}}\hat{\lambda}_{\nu}^{n}\langle\hat{\psi}_{\nu}^{n},\hat{\psi}_{\mu}^{n}\rangle_{X}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\langle\psi_{i},\hat{\psi}_{\nu}^{n}\rangle_{X}\overline{\langle\psi_{i},\hat{\psi}_{\mu}^{n}\rangle}_{X}=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{I}}\hat{\lambda}_{\mu}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\big|\langle\psi_{i},\hat{\psi}_{\mu}^{n}\rangle_{X}\big|^{2}.\end{aligned}

(2.5.4)

Next, we add a zero term by utilizing
1=\|\psi_{i}\|_{X}^{2}=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{I}}\big|\langle\psi_{i},\hat{\psi}_{\mu}^{n}\rangle_{X}\big|^{2}
:

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\Big(\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{I}}\hat{\lambda}_{\mu}^{n}\big|\langle\psi_{i},\hat{\psi}_{\mu}^{n}\rangle_{X}\big|^{2}+\hat{\lambda}_{\ell}^{n}-\hat{\lambda}_{\ell}^{n}\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{I}}\big|\langle\psi_{i},\hat{\psi}_{\mu}^{n}\rangle_{X}\big|^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\Big(\hat{\lambda}_{\ell}^{n}+\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{I}}(\hat{\lambda}_{\mu}^{n}-\hat{\lambda}_{\ell}^{n})\big|\langle\psi_{i},\hat{\psi}_{\mu}^{n}\rangle_{X}\big|^{2}\Big)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\Big(\hat{\lambda}_{\ell}^{n}+\sum_{\mu=1}^{\ell}(\hat{\lambda}_{\mu}^{n}-\hat{\lambda}_{\ell}^{n})\big|\langle\psi_{i},\hat{\psi}_{\mu}^{n}\rangle_{X}\big|^{2}\Big),

where we have used that
\hat{\lambda}_{\mu}^{n}\leq\hat{\lambda}_{\ell}^{n}
for
\mu\geq\ell
. Rearranging the sum, we finally end up with

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu=1}^{\ell}\Big(\hat{\lambda}_{\ell}^{n}+(\hat{\lambda}_{\mu}^{n}-\hat{\lambda}_{\ell}^{n})\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\big|\langle\psi_{i},\hat{\psi}_{\mu}^{n}\rangle_{X}\big|^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\mu=1}^{\ell}\Big(\hat{\lambda}_{\ell}^{n}+(\hat{\lambda}_{\mu}^{n}-\hat{\lambda}_{\ell}^{n})\sum_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\big|\langle\psi_{i},\hat{\psi}_{\mu}^{n}\rangle_{X}\big|^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu=1}^{\ell}\Big(\hat{\lambda}_{\ell}^{n}+(\hat{\lambda}_{\mu}^{n}-\hat{\lambda}_{\ell}^{n})\Big)=\sum_{\mu=1}^{\ell}\hat{\lambda}_{\mu}^{n}.

(2.5.5)

We have now reached an upper bound to the cost function
\mathscr{F}
for any orthonormal set
\{\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}\}
. If we particularly insert the set
\{\hat{\psi}_{1}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\psi}_{\ell}^{n}\}
into (
2.5.4
) the objective value is

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}(\hat{\psi}_{1}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\psi}_{\ell}^{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{I}}\hat{\lambda}_{\mu}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\big|\langle\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n},\hat{\psi}_{\mu}^{n}\rangle_{X}\big|^{2}=\sum_{\mu=1}^{\ell}\hat{\lambda}_{\mu}^{n}

which coincides with the upper bound (
2.5.5
) and proves (
2.1.16
). Thus,
\{\hat{\psi}_{1}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\psi}_{\ell}^{n}\}
is a global solution to (

^
P

ℓ

n

) and therefore also to (

P
ℓ

n

). Finally, we have to prove (
2.1.15
). In order to do this, we use (
2.1.14
) and (
2.1.16
) and get

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\Big\|y_{j}^{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\Big\|_{X}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\big|{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}

which proves (
2.1.15
).
\Box

Remark 2.5.1
.

Theorem
2.1.14
can also be proved by using the theory of non-linear programming; see
[
HLBR12
,
Vol01
]
, for instance. For that purpose we introduce the quadratic cost functional

J(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell})=-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\big|{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}\quad\text{for }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset X.

Moreover, let

e(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell})=\big(\big({\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{X}-\delta_{ij}\big)\big)\in\mathbb{C}^{\ell\times\ell}\quad\text{for }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset X.

Note that
e(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell})=e(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell})^{\mathsf{H}}
holds, i.e.,
e(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell})
is a Hermitian matrix. Now (

^
P

ℓ

n

) can be expressed as the equality constrained optimization problem

\min J(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell})\quad\text{subject to}\quad\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset X\text{ and }e(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell})=0.

(2.5.6)

Endowing the matrix space
\mathbb{C}^{\ell\times\ell}
by the inner product

\displaystyle{\langle A,B\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{\ell\times\ell}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}a_{ij}\overline{b}_{ij}\quad\text{for }A=\big(\big(a_{ij}\big)\big),\,B=\big(\big(b_{ij}\big)\big)\in\mathbb{C}^{\ell\times\ell},

the Lagrange functional associated with (
2.5.6
) is given as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell},\Lambda)=J(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell})+{\langle e(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}),\Lambda\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{\ell\times\ell}}

for
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset X\text{ and }
and
\Lambda=((\lambda_{ij}))\in\mathbb{C}^{\ell\times\ell}
. Applying a Lagrangian framework
[
NW06
, Chapter 12]
it turns out that (
2.1.10
) are first-order necessary optimality conditions for (

^
P

ℓ

n

). Especially,
\lambda_{ii}=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}
and
\lambda_{ij}=0
hold for
1\leq i,j\leq\ell
. For the special case
X=\mathbb{R}^{m}
endowed with the Euclidean inner product, for
{K}=1
and
\ell=1
first- and second-order optimality conditions are discussed in Section
2.1.8
.
\blacksquare

Proof of Lemma
2.1.16
.

1)

First we proof that
\mathcal{Y}^{n}
is bounded. Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and (
2.1.13
) we infer that

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{Y}^{n}\Phi\|}_{X}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,|\Phi_{kj}|{\|y_{j}^{k}\|}_{X}\leq\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\big|\Phi_{kj}\big|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|y_{j}^{k}\|}_{X}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\bigg)^{1/2}{\|\Phi\|}_{\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}}\quad\text{for every }\Phi=\big(\big(\Phi_{kj}\big)\big)\in\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}.

Consequently,
\mathcal{Y}^{n}
is bounded. The image space of
\mathcal{Y}^{n}
is given by
\mathscr{V}^{n}
, where
\mathscr{V}^{n}
has been defined in (
2.1.1
). Thus,
\mathcal{Y}^{n}
is a finite rank operator and therefore compact; see
[
RS80
, p. 199]
, for example.

2)

Let
\psi\in X
be arbitrary. Then it holds

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}\psi,\Phi\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}}

\displaystyle={\langle\psi,\mathcal{Y}^{n}\Phi\rangle}_{X}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\overline{\Phi}_{kj}\,{\langle\psi,y^{k}_{j}\rangle}_{X}={\langle(({\langle\psi,y^{k}_{j}\rangle}_{X})),\Phi\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}},

which gives the claim.

3)

Clearly, the composition of two linear operators is linear, too. By Lemma
2.1.16
the operator
\mathcal{Y}^{n}
is bounded, so that its adjoint
\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}
is bounded as well; see, e.g.,
[
RS80
, p. 186]
. Hence,
\mathcal{Y}^{n}\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}
is also bounded. Since
\mathcal{Y}^{n}
is compact (see Lemma
2.1.16
) and
\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}
is bounded,
\mathcal{Y}^{n}\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}
is compact; see, e.g.,
[
RS80
, p. 200]
. From
(\mathcal{Y}^{n}\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star})^{\star}=\mathcal{Y}^{n}\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}
we derive that
\mathcal{Y}^{n}\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}
is self-adjoint. As we have

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{Y}^{n}\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}\psi,\psi\rangle}_{X}={\langle\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}\psi,\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}\psi\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}}={\|\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}\psi\|}_{\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}}\geq 0\quad\text{for every }\psi\in X,

the operator
\mathcal{Y}^{n}\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}
is even non-negative. Finally, we get for any
\psi\in X

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}^{n}\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}\psi=\mathcal{Y}^{n}\Big(\big({\langle\psi,y^{k}_{j}\rangle}_{X}\big)_{kj}\Big)=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\langle\psi,y^{k}_{j}\rangle}_{X}y_{j}^{k}=\mathcal{R}^{n}\psi.

Thus,
\mathcal{R}^{n}=\mathcal{Y}^{n}\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}
holds.
\Box

Proof of Lemma
2.1.18
.
We already know that
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{d^{n}}
are orthonormal in
X
. Utilizing (
2.1.18
),
\mathcal{R}^{n}=\mathcal{Y}^{n}\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}
and (
2.1.10
) we find

\displaystyle{\langle\hat{\bm{\Phi}}_{i}^{n},\hat{\bm{\Phi}}_{j}^{n}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{n}}\,{\langle\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n},\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}\hat{\psi}_{j}^{n}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}}=\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{n}}\,{\langle\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n},\mathcal{R}^{n}\hat{\psi}_{j}^{n}\rangle}_{X}=\frac{\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{n}}{\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{n}}\,{\langle\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n},\hat{\psi}_{j}^{n}\rangle}_{X}

\displaystyle=\frac{\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{n}\delta_{ij}}{\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{n}}\quad\text{for }i,j=1,\ldots,d^{n}.

Thus, the elements
\{\hat{\Phi}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d^{n}}
are orthonormal in
\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}
. Again by (
2.1.18
),
\mathcal{R}^{n}=\mathcal{Y}^{n}\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}
and (
2.1.10
) we obtain

\mathcal{Y}^{n}\hat{\bm{\Phi}}^{n}_{i}=\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}}\mathcal{Y}^{n}\mathcal{Y}^{n,\star}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}=\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}}\mathcal{R}^{n}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}=\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,d^{n}.

Finally, (
2.1.19
) follows directly from the definition of the elements
\{\hat{\bm{\Phi}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d^{n}}
in (
2.1.18
). From (
2.1.19
) and with the fact that
\{\hat{\psi}_{1}^{n},...,\hat{\psi}_{d^{n}}^{n}\}
forms an orthonormal basis of
\mathscr{V}^{n}
, we derive directly (
2.1.20
).
\Box

Proof of Lemma
2.1.20
.
Let
(k,j)\in\{1,\ldots,{K}\}\times\{1,\ldots,n\}
be chosen arbitrarily. We define
{\bm{\Phi}}=((\Phi_{\nu l}))\in\mathbb{C}^{K\times n}
by

\displaystyle\Phi_{\nu l}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{\omega_{k}^{K}\alpha_{j}^{n}}&&\text{if }(\nu,l)=(k,j),\\
&0&&\text{otherwise}\end{aligned}\right.

for
1\leq\nu\leq K
and
1\leq l\leq n
. Since
\{\hat{\bm{\Phi}}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{{K}n}
forms an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{C}^{K\times n}
, we have the representation

\displaystyle{\bm{\Phi}}=\sum_{i=1}^{{K}n}{\langle{\bm{\Phi}},\hat{\bm{\Phi}}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}}\hat{\bm{\Phi}}_{i}^{n}.

Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\omega_{k}^{K}\alpha_{j}^{n}}=\Phi_{kj}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{{K}n}{\langle{\bm{\Phi}},\hat{\bm{\Phi}}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{{K}\times n}}\hat{\bm{\Phi}}_{i}^{n}\right)_{kj}=\sum_{i=1}^{{K}n}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=1}^{K}\omega_{\nu}^{K}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\alpha_{l}^{n}\Phi_{\nu l}\overline{(\hat{\bm{\Phi}}_{i}^{n})}_{\nu l}\bigg)\big(\hat{\bm{\Phi}}^{n}_{i}\big)_{kj}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{{K}n}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=1}^{K}\omega_{\nu}^{K}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\alpha_{l}^{n}\,\frac{\delta_{k\nu}\delta_{jl}}{\omega_{k}^{K}\alpha_{j}^{n}}\,\overline{(\hat{\bm{\Phi}}_{i}^{n})}_{\nu l}\bigg)\big(\hat{\bm{\Phi}}^{n}_{i}\big)_{kj}=\sum_{i=1}^{{K}n}\big|\big(\hat{\bm{\Phi}}^{n}_{i}\big)_{kj}\big|^{2}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\omega_{k}^{K}\alpha_{j}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{{K}n}\big|\big(\hat{\bm{\Phi}}^{n}_{i}\big)_{kj}\big|^{2}=1

for every
(k,j)\in\{1,\ldots,{K}\}\times\{1,\ldots,n\}
. This implies

\displaystyle\Big\|y_{j}^{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\Big\|_{X}^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big\|\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n}\overline{\big(\hat{\bm{\Phi}}_{i}^{n}\big)}_{kj}\,\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\Big\|_{X}^{2}=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}(\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{n})^{2}\big|\big(\hat{\bm{\Phi}}_{i}^{n}\big)_{kj}\big|^{2}\leq(\hat{\sigma}_{\ell+1}^{n})^{2}\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\big|\big(\hat{\bm{\Phi}}_{i}^{n}\big)_{kj}\big|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(\hat{\sigma}_{\ell+1}^{n})^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{{K}n}\big|\big(\hat{\bm{\Phi}}_{i}^{n}\big)_{kj}\big|^{2}=\frac{(\hat{\sigma}_{\ell+1}^{n})^{2}}{\omega_{k}^{K}\alpha_{j}^{n}},

which implies (
2.1.21
).
\Box

Proof of Corollary
2.1.24
.

1)

Optimality of the POD basis
: We have

\displaystyle{\|Y-\hat{\Psi}^{\ell}\hat{B}^{\ell}\|}_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\Big|y_{kj}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j},\hat{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\hat{\psi}_{ki}\Big|^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\|y_{j}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j},\hat{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\hat{\psi}_{i}\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}^{2},

and with the same argument

\displaystyle{\|Y-\Psi^{\ell}B^{\ell}\|}_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\|y_{j}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\psi_{i}\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}^{2}.

Since
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
solves (
2.1.26
), we immediately get

\displaystyle{\|Y-\hat{\Psi}^{\ell}\hat{B}^{\ell}\|}_{F}\leq{\|Y-\Psi^{\ell}B^{\ell}\|}_{F},

which was the claim.

2)

The claim follows from (
2.1.27
) and
\langle\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n},\hat{\psi}_{k}^{n}\rangle_{X}=\delta_{ik}
for
1\leq i,k\leq\ell
:

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\overline{\langle y_{j},\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}{\langle y_{j},\hat{\psi}_{k}^{n}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle\sum_{j=1}^{n}{\langle\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n},y_{j}\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\,y_{j},\hat{\psi}_{k}^{n}\bigg\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}={\langle YY^{\mathsf{H}}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n},\hat{\psi}_{k}^{n}\big\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}={\langle\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\hat{\psi}_{i}^{n},\hat{\psi}_{k}^{n}\big\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}

\displaystyle=\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\delta_{ik}\quad\text{for }1\leq i,k\leq\ell.

2.5.2
Proofs of Section
2.2

Proof of Lemma
2.2.4
.

1)

Utilizing the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and
y^{k}\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D};X)
for
1\leq k\leq{K}
we infer that

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{Y}\phi\|}_{X}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\big|\phi^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\big|{\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\|}_{X}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\,{\|\phi^{k}\|}_{L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C})}{\|y^{k}\|}_{L^{2}(\mathscr{D};X)}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\,{\|\phi^{k}\|}_{L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C})}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\,{\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\|}_{X}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}=C_{\mathcal{Y}}\,{\|\phi\|}_{L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K})}

for any
\phi\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K})
, where we set
C_{\mathcal{Y}}=(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\,{\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\|}_{X}^{2})^{1/2}<\infty
. Hence,
\mathcal{Y}
is bounded.

2)

The Hilbert space adjoint
\mathcal{Y}^{\star}:X\to L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K})
of
\mathcal{Y}
satisfies

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{Y}^{\star}\psi,\phi\rangle}_{L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K})}={\langle\psi,\mathcal{Y}\phi\rangle}_{X}\quad\text{for }\psi\in X\text{ and }\phi\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K}).

Since we derive

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{Y}^{\star}\psi,\phi\rangle}_{L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K})}

\displaystyle={\langle\psi,\mathcal{Y}\phi\rangle}_{X}=\bigg\langle\psi,\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega^{K}_{k}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\phi^{k}({\bm{\mu}})y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\bigg\rangle_{X}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega^{K}_{k}\int_{\mathscr{D}}{\langle\psi,y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{X}\overline{\phi^{k}({\bm{\mu}})}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}=\left\langle{\big({\langle\psi,y^{k}(\cdot)\rangle}_{X}\big)}_{1\leq k\leq{K}},\phi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K})}

for
\psi\in X
and
\phi\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K})
, the adjoint operator is given by (
2.2.4
). Next we prove that
\mathcal{Y}^{\star}
is compact. Let
\{\chi_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset X
be a sequence converging weakly to an element
\chi\in X
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}{\langle\chi_{n},\psi\rangle}_{X}={\langle\chi,\psi\rangle}_{X}\quad\text{for all }\psi\in X.

This implies that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}(\mathcal{Y}^{\star}\chi_{n})(t)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\langle\chi_{n},y^{1}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{X}\\
\vdots\\
{\langle\chi_{n},y^{K}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{X}\\
\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\langle\chi,y^{1}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{X}\\
\vdots\\
{\langle\chi,y^{K}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{X}\\
\end{array}\right)=\big(\mathcal{Y}^{\star}\chi\big)(t)

for
{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathscr{D}
a.e. Thus, the sequence
\{\mathcal{Y}^{\star}\chi_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges weakly to
\mathcal{Y}^{\star}\chi
in
L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}^{K})
.
\Box

Proof of Corollary
2.2.5
.

1)

From

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\star}\big)\psi=\mathcal{Y}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\langle\psi,y^{1}(\cdot)\rangle}_{X}\\
\vdots\\
{\langle\psi,y^{K}(\cdot)\rangle}_{X}\\
\end{array}\right)=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}{\langle\psi,y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{X}y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\quad\text{for }\psi\in X

and (
2.2.2
) we infer that
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\star}
holds. Due to Lemma
2.2.4
the operator
\mathcal{Y}
is bounded and its adjoint
\mathcal{Y}^{\star}
even compact. Consequently,
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\star}
is compact. We have
\mathcal{R}^{\star}=(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\star})^{\star}=\mathcal{R}
, i.e., the operator
\mathcal{R}
is self-adjoint. From

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{R}\psi,\psi\rangle}_{X}

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}{\langle\psi,y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{X}\,y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}},\psi\bigg\rangle_{X}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\big|{\langle\psi,y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\geq 0\quad\text{for all }\psi\in X

we infer that
\mathcal{R}
is non-negative.

2)

It follows from the proof of Lemma
2.2.5
that
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{Y}^{\star}\mathcal{Y}
is compact. However, the compactness of
\mathcal{K}
can also be shown as follows: Notice that the kernel function

\displaystyle r_{ik}:\mathscr{D}\times\mathscr{D}\to\mathbb{C},\quad r_{ik}({\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\mu}})={\langle y^{k}({\bm{\nu}}),y^{i}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{X}\quad\text{for }1\leq i,k\leq{K},

belongs to
L^{2}(\mathscr{D}\times\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C})
. Then it follows from
[
Yos95
, pp. 197 and 277]
that the linear integral operator
\mathcal{K}_{ik}:L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C})\to L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C})
defined by

\displaystyle(\mathcal{K}_{ik}\phi)({\bm{\mu}})=\int_{\mathscr{D}}r_{ik}({\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\mu}})\phi({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}\quad\text{for }\phi\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{C}),

is a compact operator. This implies that the operator
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathcal{K}_{ik}
is compact for
1\leq i\leq{K}
as well.
\Box

Proof of Theorem
2.2.7
.
The existence of sequences
\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
of eigenvalues and
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
of associated eigenfunctions satisfying (
2.2.5
) follows from Corrolary
2.2.5
-1), Theorem
A.3.49
and Theorem
A.3.50
. Analogous to the proof of Theorem
2.1.14
one can show that
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
solves (

P
ℓ

) as well as (

^
P

ℓ

) and that (
2.2.6
) and (
2.2.7
), respectively, are valid.
\Box

Proof of Lemma
2.2.4
.

1)

In fact,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\hat{\psi}_{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}{\langle\hat{\psi}_{i},y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{X}\,y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\quad\text{for every }i\in\mathbb{I}.

Taking the inner product with
\hat{\psi}_{i}
and using (
2.2.5
) we get

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\big|{\langle\hat{\psi}_{i},y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}={\langle\mathcal{R}\hat{\psi}_{i},\hat{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{X}=\hat{\lambda}_{i},

which gives (
2.2.8
). Obviously, (
2.1.11
) and
\hat{\lambda}_{i}=0
for
i>d
imply (
2.2.9
).

2)

Expanding each
y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\in X
in terms of
\{\hat{\psi}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
for each
1\leq k\leq{K}
we have

\displaystyle y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}{\langle y^{k}({\bm{\mu}}),\hat{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\hat{\psi}_{i}

and hence

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}{\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\|}_{X}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\big|{\langle y^{k}({\bm{\mu}}),\hat{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\hat{\lambda}_{i},

which is (
2.2.10
).
\Box

2.5.3
Proofs of Section
2.3

Proof of Theorem
2.3.1
.
We employ a proof by induction over
i
, starting at
i=1
. Our first step is to isolate the eigenvalue
\hat{\lambda}_{1}>0
from the rest of the spectrum by a closed curve. Since all occuring eigenvalues are real, we will always choose the separating curves as the boundaries of rectangles in the complex plane

\displaystyle R_{[a,b]}=[a,b]+i[-1,1]=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}~|~\Re(z)\in[a,b],\Im(z)\in[-1,1]\right\}\quad\text{for }a,b\in\mathbb{R}

and denote the curve by
\Gamma_{[a,b]}=\partial R_{[a,b]}
. For a first curve, let us choose
\Gamma^{(1)}:=\Gamma_{[\hat{\lambda}_{1}-\varepsilon,\hat{\lambda}_{1}+\varepsilon]}
. By the ordering of eigenvalues, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}_{1}=\ldots=\hat{\lambda}_{r}>\hat{\lambda}_{r+1},

where
r\in\mathbb{N}
is the multiplicity of
\hat{\lambda}_{1}
. Therefore, if
\varepsilon
is chosen small enough,
\hat{\lambda}_{1}
is the only spectral point of
\mathcal{R}
in
\Gamma^{(1)}
. As
\mathcal{R}^{n}\to\mathcal{R}
as
n\to\infty
, we can apply Theorem
A.3.54
to
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{R}
,
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{R}^{n}
,
\Sigma=\{\hat{\lambda}_{1}\}
and
\Sigma^{\prime}=\{\hat{\lambda}_{i}\}_{i>r}
for large enough
n
. This means that for such
n
, the spectrum of
\mathcal{R}^{n}
is also split in two parts
\Sigma(\mathcal{R}^{n})
inside of
\Gamma^{(1)}
and
\Sigma^{\prime}(\mathcal{R}^{n})
outside of
\Gamma^{(1)}
. In the according decompositions
X=M(\mathcal{R})\oplus M^{\prime}(\mathcal{R})
and
X=M(\mathcal{R}^{n})\oplus M^{\prime}(\mathcal{R}^{n})
from Theorem
A.3.51
, property 2) states that
M(\mathcal{R})
and
M(\mathcal{R}^{n})
are isomorphic, so in particular of same dimension. Since
\mathcal{R}
is compact and self-adjoint, we may employ Corollary
A.3.52
which states that
M(\mathcal{R})
is given by the eigenspace to
\hat{\lambda}_{1}
. The operator
\mathcal{R}^{n}
is likewise compact and self-adjoint, so by Remark
A.3.53

\Sigma(\mathcal{R}^{n})
consists of at most
r
different eigenvalues
\hat{\lambda}^{n}_{j_{1}^{n}},\ldots,\hat{\lambda}^{n}_{j_{r}^{n}}
of
\mathcal{R}^{n}
contained in
\Gamma^{(1)}
and the
r
-dimensional space
M(\mathcal{R}^{n})
consists of eigenvectors to these eigenvalues. If we now send
\varepsilon\to 0
, it follows

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}_{j_{1}^{n}}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\lambda}_{j_{r}^{n}}^{n}\to\hat{\lambda}_{1}\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

We would like to show that these eigenvalues can only be given by
\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\lambda}_{r}^{n}
. To do this, we consider the fixed curve

\displaystyle\tilde{\Gamma}^{(1)}=\Gamma_{[\nicefrac{{3\hat{\lambda}_{2}}}{{4}}+\nicefrac{{\hat{\lambda}_{1}}}{{4}},\hat{\lambda}_{1}+1]}.

Obviously,
\hat{\lambda}_{1}
is still the only eigenvalue of
\mathcal{R}
enclosed by
\tilde{\Gamma}^{(1)}
and
\tilde{\Gamma}^{(1)}
encloses
\Gamma^{(1)}
for small enough
\varepsilon
. If
n
is large enough, Theorem
A.3.54
again states that there are at most
r
different eigenvalues of
\mathcal{R}^{n}
inside
\tilde{\Gamma}^{(1)}
which can only be given by
\hat{\lambda}_{j_{1}^{n}}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\lambda}_{j_{r}^{n}}^{n}
. Also, for large enough
n
,
\mathcal{R}^{n}
has no more eigenvalues larger than
\hat{\lambda}_{1}+1
because of
\|\mathcal{R}^{n}-\mathcal{R}\|_{\mathscr{L}(X)}\to 0
. So the
r
largest eigenvalues (counting with multiplicity) of
\mathcal{R}^{n}
are enclosed by
\tilde{\Gamma}^{(1)}
. In other words,
\hat{\lambda}_{j_{1}^{n}}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\lambda}_{j_{r}^{n}}^{n}
are given by
\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\lambda}_{r}^{n}
for large enough
n
. This proves
\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\lambda}_{r}^{n}\to\hat{\lambda}_{1}
. Also,
\hat{\psi}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\psi}_{r}
is an orthonormal basis of
M(\mathcal{R})
and
\hat{\psi}_{1}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\psi}_{r}^{n}
is an orthonormal basis of
M(\mathcal{R}^{n})
if
n
is large enough. Therefore, the projections
P(\mathcal{R})
and
P(\mathcal{R}^{n})
onto
M(\mathcal{R})
and
M(\mathcal{R}^{n})
from Theorem
A.3.54
-3) are given by

\displaystyle P(\mathcal{R})\psi=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\langle\psi,\hat{\psi}_{j}\rangle_{X}\hat{\psi}_{j},\quad P(\mathcal{R}^{n})\psi=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\langle\psi,\hat{\psi}_{j}^{n}\rangle_{X}\hat{\psi}_{j}^{n}\quad\text{for }\psi\in X.

Because of this the convergence result (
2.3.3b
) for
\ell=r
follows directly from the fact that
P(\mathcal{R}^{n})\to P(\mathcal{R})
by Theorem
A.3.54
. This concludes the induction base.

For the induction step, let us assume that we are given a certain index
i
which shall satisfy
\hat{\lambda}_{i}<\hat{\lambda}_{i-1}
without loss of generality. Like in the induction base, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}_{i}=\ldots=\hat{\lambda}_{i+r-1}>\hat{\lambda}_{i+r}.

For the sake of simplicity, we have used the same
r
as in the induction base, even though they may be different. Again, let us choose a closed curve
\Gamma^{(i)}=\Gamma_{[\hat{\lambda}_{i}-\varepsilon,\hat{\lambda}_{i}+\varepsilon]}
where
\varepsilon>0
is chosen small enough such that
\hat{\lambda}_{i-1}
and
\hat{\lambda}_{i+r}
lie on the outside of
\Gamma^{(i)}
. Similar to the induction base, we argue that if
n
is large enough,
\mathcal{R}^{n}
has at most
r
different eigenvalues
\hat{\lambda}_{j_{1}^{n}},\ldots,\hat{\lambda}_{j_{r}^{n}}^{n}
inside
\Gamma^{(i)}
. By the induction hypothesis, we know that
\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\lambda}_{i-1}^{n}
converge to
\hat{\lambda}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\lambda}_{i-1}
which lie outside of
\Gamma^{(i)}
by construction, so
\hat{\lambda}_{j_{1}^{n}}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\lambda}_{j_{r}^{n}}^{n}
have to be distinct from those if
n
is large enough. Again by induction, we know that the left-hand boundary of
\tilde{\Gamma}^{(i-1)}
was chosen as
\tfrac{3}{4}\hat{\lambda}_{i}+\tfrac{1}{4}\hat{\lambda}_{i-1}
and that for large enough
n
, the only eigenvalues of
\mathcal{R}^{n}
inside
\tilde{\Gamma}^{(i-1)}
are
\hat{\lambda}_{i-q}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\lambda}_{i-1}^{n}
(if
q
was the multiplicity of
\hat{\lambda}_{i-1}
). If we now choose the new curve as

\displaystyle\tilde{\Gamma}^{(i)}:=\Gamma_{[\nicefrac{{3\hat{\lambda}_{i+1}}}{{4}}+\nicefrac{{\hat{\lambda}_{i}}}{{4}},\nicefrac{{(\hat{\lambda}_{i}+\hat{\lambda}_{i-1})}}{{2}}]}

we have achieved that the interiors of
\tilde{\Gamma}^{(i-1)}
and
\tilde{\Gamma}^{(i)}
overlap, cf. Figure
2.5.1
.

\hat{\lambda}_{i}
\hat{\lambda}_{i-1}
\tilde{\Gamma}^{(i)}
\tilde{\Gamma}^{(i-1)}
\tfrac{3}{4}\hat{\lambda}_{i+1}+\tfrac{1}{4}\hat{\lambda}_{i}
\tfrac{1}{2}(\hat{\lambda}_{i}+\hat{\lambda}_{i-1})
\tfrac{3}{4}\hat{\lambda}_{i}+\tfrac{1}{4}\hat{\lambda}_{i-1}
\tfrac{1}{2}(\hat{\lambda}_{i-1}+\hat{\lambda}_{i-2})
\Re
\Im

Figure 2.5.1
:
Choice of the curves
\tilde{\Gamma}^{(i)}
and
\tilde{\Gamma}^{(i-1)}
in the proof of Theorem
2.3.1
.

Because of this overlap, we can conclude that the united interiors of
\tilde{\Gamma}^{(i-1)}
and
\tilde{\Gamma}^{(i)}
contain exactly the
\mathcal{R}^{n}
-eigenvalues
\hat{\lambda}_{i-q}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\lambda}_{i-1}^{n}
and
\hat{\lambda}_{j_{1}^{n}}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\lambda}_{j_{r}^{n}}^{n}
. In other words,
\hat{\lambda}_{j_{1}^{n}},\ldots,\hat{\lambda}_{j_{r}^{n}}^{n}
can only be the largest
r
eigenvalues which are smaller than
\hat{\lambda}_{i-1}^{n}
, i.e.,
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n},\hat{\lambda}_{i+1}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\lambda}_{i+r-1}
. Altogether, we have shown that
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n},\ldots,\hat{\lambda}_{i+r}^{n}\to\hat{\lambda}_{i}
as
n\to\infty
.

Completely analogue to the induction base, it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=i}^{i+r}\langle\psi,\hat{\psi}_{j}^{n}\rangle_{X}\hat{\psi}_{j}^{n}~\to~\sum_{j=i}^{i+r}\langle\psi,\hat{\psi}_{j}\rangle_{X}\hat{\psi}_{j}\quad\text{for all }\psi\in X\text{ as }n\to\infty.

Together with the induction hypothesis, (
2.3.3b
) follows for
\ell=i+r
. This concludes the induction part of the proof. We still have to show (
2.3.3a
).

We first observe that
y^{k}\in C(\mathscr{D};X)\subset L^{1}(\mathscr{D};X)
since
\mathscr{D}
is compact in
\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{p}}
. This together with (
2.3.1
) implies:

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{k}\,{\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}}_{j})\|}_{X}^{2}\to\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}{\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\|}_{X}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

(2.5.7)

If we utilize (
2.1.13
) for the left and (
2.2.10
) for the right, we can formulate (
2.5.7
) equivalently as

\sum_{i=1}^{d^{n}}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\to\sum_{i=1}^{d}\hat{\lambda}_{i}\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

(2.5.8)

Now choose and fix
\ell
such that
\hat{\lambda}_{\ell}\neq\hat{\lambda}_{\ell+1}
for which we have already shown that
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{n}\to\hat{\lambda}_{i}
for all
1\leq\ell
. This together with (
2.5.8
) finally proves (
2.3.3a
).
\Box

Proof of Proposition
2.3.4
.

For an arbitrary
\psi\in X
with
\|\psi\|_{X}=1
we define
F:\mathscr{D}\to X
by

\displaystyle F({\bm{\mu}})=\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\langle y^{k}({\bm{\mu}}),\psi\rangle}_{X}\,y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\quad\text{for }{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathscr{D}.

It follows that

\mathcal{R}\psi=\int_{\mathscr{D}}F({\bm{\mu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}=\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{a}}}^{{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{b}}}F({\bm{\mu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}F({\bm{\mu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}.

(2.5.9)

The trapezoidal approximation of
\mathcal{R}\psi
reads

\mathcal{R}^{n}\psi=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}F({\bm{\mu}}_{j}).

(2.5.10)

Next, we infer from
\|\psi\|_{X}=1
that

{\|F({\bm{\mu}})\|}_{X}^{2}\leq\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\|}_{X}^{2}\bigg)^{2}.

(2.5.11)

Now, we show that
F
belongs to
H^{1}(\mathscr{D};X)
and its norm is bounded independently of
\psi
. By assumption,
y^{k}\in C(\mathscr{D};X)
holds for
1\leq k\leq{K}
. Furthermore,
[0,T]
is bounded. Thus,
y^{k}\in L^{4}(\mathscr{D};X)
which yields

\displaystyle{\|F\|}_{L^{2}(\mathscr{D};X)}^{2}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\|y^{k}\|}_{L^{4}(\mathscr{D};X)}^{4}=C_{1}.

Moreover, it holds

\displaystyle F_{\bm{\mu}}({\bm{\mu}})=\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\langle y^{k}_{{\bm{\mu}}}({\bm{\mu}}),\psi\rangle}_{X}\,y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})+{\langle y^{k}({\bm{\mu}}),\psi\rangle}_{X}\,y^{k}_{\bm{\mu}}({\bm{\mu}})\quad\text{f.a.a. }{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathscr{D},

where
f.a.a.
stands for
for almost all
. Since the set
\mathscr{D}
is one-dimensional, we have
H^{1}(\mathscr{D};X)\hookrightarrow C(\mathscr{D};X)
; see
[
Eva08
, p. 269]
, for instance. so that we have
y^{k}\in C(\mathscr{D};X)
. Thus, we derive

\displaystyle{\|F_{\bm{\mu}}({\bm{\mu}})\|}_{X}\leq 2\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\|}_{X}{\|y^{k}_{\bm{\mu}}({\bm{\mu}})\|}_{X}

which implies

\displaystyle{\|F_{\bm{\mu}}\|}_{L^{2}(\mathscr{D};X)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\int_{\mathscr{D}}\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\|}_{X}{\|y^{k}_{\bm{\mu}}({\bm{\mu}})\|}_{X}\bigg)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\leq 4{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\nu=1}^{K}\int_{\mathscr{D}}{\|y^{k}({\bm{\mu}})\|}_{X}^{2}{\|y^{\nu}_{\bm{\mu}}({\bm{\mu}})\|}_{X}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}

\displaystyle\leq 4{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\|y^{k}\|}_{C(\mathscr{D};X)}^{2}\sum_{\nu=1}^{K}{\|y^{\nu}_{\bm{\mu}}\|}_{L^{2}(\mathscr{D};X)}^{2}=C_{2},

where we have utilized that
y^{k}\in C(\mathscr{D};X)
for
1\leq k\leq{K}
. Consequently,

{\|F\|}_{H^{1}(\mathscr{D};X)}=\bigg({\|F\|}_{L^{2}(\mathscr{D};X)}^{2}+{\|F_{\bm{\mu}}\|}_{L^{2}(\mathscr{D};X)}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\leq C_{3}

(2.5.12)

with the constant
C_{3}=(C_{1}+C_{2})^{1/2}
, which is independent of
\psi
. For the next equality, we utilize the fundamental theorem of calculus for the function
F
:

\displaystyle\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{\bm{\mu}}F_{\bm{\nu}}({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}=F({\bm{\mu}})-F({\bm{\mu}}_{j})\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}^{{\bm{\mu}}}F_{\bm{\nu}}({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}=F({\bm{\mu}})-F({\bm{\mu}}_{j+1})

for
{\bm{\mu}}\in[{\bm{\mu}}_{j},{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}]
for
j=1,\ldots,n-1
. Thus, we derive for the integral on the
j
-th subinterval:

\displaystyle\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}F({\bm{\mu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}\Big(F({\bm{\mu}}_{j})+\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{\bm{\mu}}F_{\bm{\nu}}({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}\Big)+\Big(F({\bm{\mu}}_{j+1})+\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}^{\bm{\mu}}F_{\bm{\nu}}({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}\Big)\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}

(2.5.13)

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}{2}\,\big(F({\bm{\mu}}_{j})+F({\bm{\mu}}_{j+1})\big)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\,\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}\Big(\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{\bm{\mu}}F_{\bm{\nu}}({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}+\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}^{\bm{\mu}}F_{\bm{\nu}}({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}\Big)\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}.

Combining (
2.5.9
) and (
2.5.13
) together with the definition for
\alpha_{j}^{n}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\psi

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}F({\bm{\mu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}F({\bm{\mu}}_{j})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}\Big(\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{\bm{\mu}}F_{\bm{\nu}}({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}+\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}^{\bm{\mu}}F_{\bm{\nu}}({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}\Big)\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}

This concludes the final representation of
\mathcal{R}\psi
. Let us mention that the discrete variant can also be represented by the function
F
since
\mathcal{R}^{n}\psi=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}F({\bm{\mu}}_{j})
. Inserting these terms into (
2.5.10
), we obtain

\displaystyle{\big\|\mathcal{R}^{n}\psi-\mathcal{R}\psi\big\|}_{X}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}\Big(\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{\bm{\mu}}F_{\bm{\nu}}({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}+\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}^{\bm{\mu}}F_{\bm{\nu}}({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}\Big)\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\bigg\|_{X}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\bigg\|\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{\bm{\mu}}F_{\bm{\nu}}({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\bigg\|_{X}+\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\bigg\|\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}^{\bm{\mu}}F_{\bm{\nu}}({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\bigg\|_{X}.

From the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (cf. Lemma
A.2.40
) we deduce that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\bigg\|\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{\bm{\mu}}F_{\bm{\nu}}({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\bigg\|_{X}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}\bigg\|\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{\bm{\mu}}F_{\bm{\nu}}({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}\bigg\|_{X}\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}

(2.5.14)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\Delta{\bm{\mu}}}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\bigg(\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}\Big\|\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{\bm{\mu}}F_{\bm{\nu}}({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}\Big\|_{X}^{2}\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\Delta{\bm{\mu}}}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\bigg(\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}\Big(\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{\bm{\mu}}{\|F_{\bm{\nu}}({\bm{\nu}})\|}_{X}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}\Big)^{2}\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\Delta{\bm{\mu}}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\bigg(\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{\bm{\mu}}{\|F_{\bm{\nu}}({\bm{\nu}})\|}_{X}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\Delta{\bm{\mu}}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\bigg((\mu_{j+1}-\mu_{j}){\|F\|}_{H^{1}(\mu_{j},\mu_{j+1};X)}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\Delta{\bm{\mu}}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(\mu_{j+1}-\mu_{j})\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}{\|F\|}_{H^{1}(\mu_{j},\mu_{j+1};X)}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\Delta{\bm{\mu}}\sqrt{{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{b}}-{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{a}}}\,{\|F\|}_{H^{1}(\mathscr{D};X)}.

Analogously, we derive

\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\bigg\|\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j}}^{{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}\int_{{\bm{\mu}}_{j+1}}^{\bm{\mu}}F_{\bm{\nu}}({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\bigg\|_{X}\leq\Delta{\bm{\mu}}\sqrt{{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{b}}-{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{a}}}\,{\|F\|}_{H^{1}(\mathscr{D};X)}.

(2.5.15)

From (
2.5.12
), (
2.5.14
) and (
2.5.15
) it follows that

\displaystyle{\big\|\mathcal{R}^{n}\psi-\mathcal{R}\psi\big\|}_{X}\leq C_{4}\Delta{\bm{\mu}},

where
C_{4}=C_{3}({\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{b}}-{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{a}})^{1/2}
is independent of
n
and
\psi
. Since
\Delta{\bm{\mu}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, we conclude that

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{R}^{n}-\mathcal{R}\|}_{\mathscr{L}(X)}=\sup_{\|\psi\|_{X}=1}{\|\mathcal{R}^{n}\psi-\mathcal{R}\psi\|}_{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0,

which gives the claim.
\Box

2.5.4
Proofs of Section
2.4

Proof of Proposition
2.4.8
.
Since
H^{\ell}
is a finite-dimensional subspace, it is also closed in the Hilbert space
H
, so Theorem
A.2.39
yields
H=H^{\ell}\oplus(H^{\ell})^{\perp}
. Therefore, every element
\varphi\in H
decomposes into
\varphi=\varphi^{\ell}+\varphi^{\perp}
with
\varphi^{\ell}\in H^{\ell}
and
\varphi^{\perp}\in(H^{\ell})^{\perp}
. Obviously,
\{\psi_{1}^{H},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}^{H}\}
is an
H
-orthonormal basis of
H^{\ell}
, so that we can write
\varphi^{\ell}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\langle\varphi,\psi_{i}^{H}\rangle_{H}\psi_{i}^{H}
. Lastly, Theorem
A.2.39
also states that
\varphi^{\ell}
solves the minimization problem in (
2.4.6a
), so it holds
\mathcal{P}_{H}^{\ell}\varphi=\varphi^{\ell}
. Analogously, one can prove that
\mathcal{P}_{V}^{\ell}
,
\mathcal{Q}_{H}^{\ell}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{V}
are well-defined. To prove (
2.4.8b
) we express
\varphi^{\ell}
through the basis
\{\psi_{1}^{H},...,\psi_{\ell}^{H}\}
as
\varphi^{\ell}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{v}_{i}\psi_{i}^{H}
. Taking the inner product of
\varphi
with a basis element
\psi_{i}^{H}
yields

\displaystyle{\langle\varphi,\psi_{i}^{H}\rangle}_{V}={\langle\varphi^{\ell},\psi_{i}^{H}\rangle}_{V}=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}{\langle\psi_{j}^{H},\psi_{i}^{H}\rangle}_{V}\,\mathrm{u}_{j}

which proves (
2.4.8b
). The representation (
2.4.8a
) is shown in the same way. Finally, Remark
2.4.5
yields the unique solvability of (
2.4.8b
) and (
2.4.8a
). The linearity and boundedness follows from the fact that the operators are orthogonal projections.
\Box

Chapter 3
Reduced-Order Modeling for Evolution Problems

In this chapter we consider linear and non-linear evolution problems which can be considered as dynamical systems in appropriate function spaces. For the numerical solution, a Galerkin approximation is introduced which leads to finite-dimensional linear or non-linear equation systems. However, in many applications these systems turn out to be of very large scale. For this reason reduced-order modeling is utilized to derive low-dimensional Galerkin schemes for the equation systems. In this book we apply the POD method in order to realize the model reduction. Of course, other methods, e.g., the reduced basis method can also be utilized. To ensure the validity of the so-called POD Galerkin approximation, a-priori and a-posteriori error estimates are derived. The chapter is organized as follows …

\blacksquare

In Section
3.1
, we return to the parabolic example presented in Section
1.2
involving already control input functions. The PDE is formulated as an abstract evolution equation using (bi-)linear forms.

\blacksquare

This specific form of evolution equations is studied in Section
3.2
. We present (standard) sufficient conditions ensuring existence of a unique solution for any fixed control input functions. This solution can be written as a sum of a particular solution, which depends on the given right-hand side and the initial condition, and of a control-dependent solution. The latter can be expressed as the application of a linear and bounded control-to-state operator to the chosen fixed control input function.

\blacksquare

In Section
3.3
, we analyze the continuous POD method for the evolution problem, giving us POD vectors which are built by the solution trajectory. Using these POD vectors, we introduce a Galerkin scheme by which we project the evolution equation onto the POD subspace. In an a-priori analysis, we present error bounds between the so-called truth solution and the reduced POD solution.

\blacksquare

In Section
3.4
the results from Sections
3.2
and
3.3
are transferred to a
semidiscrete approximation
, where the evolution problem is discretized by a Galerkin scheme. We present an
a-priori
and an
a-posteriori error analysis
to estimate the distance between the exact (unknown) soltution to the evolution problem to the associated solution to the semidiscrete approximation for any fixed control input function. In particular, the a-posteriori error estimates allow for a computation of the distance provided an approximate solution is available.

\blacksquare

In Section
3.5
, the temporal discretization of the semidiscrete approximation is carried out. This leads to a fully discretized scheme for the evolution problem that can be realized on a computer. All results from the previous sections carry over in an analogous way, which is carried out in the section.

\blacksquare

Section
3.6
introduces us to the more complex case when a non-linearity enters the evolution equation. Under certain assumptions, we can present a-priori and a-posteriori estimators which are similar to the linear situation from the first sections in the continuous, semidiscrete and fully discrete cases.

\blacksquare

In Section
3.6
, we explain how POD can be applied to elliptic problems. An a-priori error analysis is also presented.

\blacksquare

To improve the readability of the chapter, all proofs have been moved to Section
3.8
.

3.1
Motivating linear evolution problem

Let us return to our general numerical example from Section
1.2
in which we have encountered the
parabolic partial differential equation
(
1.2.1
) which we will quickly repeat here:

\displaystyle y_{t}(t,{\bm{x}})-\kappa\Delta y(t,{\bm{x}})+{\bm{v}}({\bm{x}})\cdot\nabla y(t,{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=f(t,{\bm{x}})+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}}u^{\mathsf{d}}_{i}(t)\chi_{i}({\bm{x}}),

\displaystyle(t,{\bm{x}})\in Q,

(3.1.1)

\displaystyle\kappa\frac{\partial y}{\partial{\bm{n}}}(t,{\bm{s}})+q({\bm{s}})y(t,{\bm{s}})

\displaystyle=g(t,{\bm{s}})+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}}u^{\mathsf{b}}_{j}(t)\xi_{j}({\bm{s}}),

\displaystyle(t,{\bm{s}})\in\Sigma,

\displaystyle y(0,{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=y_{\circ}({\bm{x}}),

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\bm{x}}\in\Omega.

We will now define what constitutes as a solution of (
3.1.1
) by using appropriate function spaces. We assume that the initial heat distribution
y_{\circ}
as well as the distributed and boundary heat sources
f,g
are given bounded functions. The input or control variable
u=(u^{\mathsf{d}},u^{\mathsf{b}})
belongs to the Hilbert space
{\mathscr{U}}=L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{m_{\mathsf{d}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{\mathsf{b}}})
endowed with the inner product

\displaystyle{\langle u,\tilde{u}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}=\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}}u_{i}^{\mathsf{d}}(t)\tilde{u}_{i}^{\mathsf{d}}(t)+\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}}u_{i}^{\mathsf{b}}(t)\tilde{u}_{i}^{\mathsf{b}}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\quad\text{for }u=(u^{\mathsf{d}},u^{\mathsf{b}}),~\tilde{u}=(\tilde{u}^{\mathsf{d}},\tilde{u}^{\mathsf{b}})\in{\mathscr{U}}

and the induced norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{U}}=\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle_{\mathscr{U}}^{1/2}
. Furthermore, the shape functions
\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}
,
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}}
are bounded. The advection term
{\bm{v}}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{n}}
is supposed to be bounded in each component
v_{i}
,
i=1,...,\mathfrak{n}
.

We are interested in a weak solution
y
to (
3.1.1
). Let
H=L^{2}(\Omega)
and
V=H^{1}(\Omega)
. Then
H
and
V
are two separable Hilbert spaces with
V\subset H
; cf. Remark
2.4.2
. Moreover,
V
is dense in
H
with compact embedding. Identifying the Hilbert space
H
with its dual space
H^{\prime}
, we get the Gelfand triple

\displaystyle V\hookrightarrow H\simeq H^{\prime}\hookrightarrow V^{\prime}.

We assume

\displaystyle y\in H^{1,1}(Q)=L^{2}(0,T;V)\cap H^{1}(0,T;H),

where
H^{1,1}(Q)
is endowed with the inner product

\displaystyle{\langle\varphi,\phi\rangle}_{H^{1,1}(Q)}

\displaystyle={\langle\varphi,\phi\rangle}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}+{\langle\varphi,\phi\rangle}_{H^{1}(0,T;H)}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}{\langle\varphi(t),\phi(t)\rangle}_{V}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}{\langle\varphi(t),\phi(t)\rangle}_{H}+{\langle\varphi_{t}(t),\phi_{t}(t)\rangle}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}t\quad\text{for }\varphi,\phi\in H^{1,1}(Q),

and the associated induced norm

\displaystyle{\|\varphi\|}_{H^{1,1}(Q)}=\left({\|\varphi\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}+{\|\varphi\|}_{H^{1}(0,T;H)}\right)^{1/2}\quad\text{for }\varphi\in H^{1,1}(Q).

Next, we multiply the first line in (
3.1.1
) by a test function
\varphi\in V
and utilize the integration by parts formula. From the boundary condition in (
3.1.1
) it follows that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}f(t,{\bm{x}})\varphi({\bm{x}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}+\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}u_{i}^{\mathsf{d}}(t)\int_{\Omega}\chi_{i}({\bm{x}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}y_{t}(t,{\bm{x}})\varphi({\bm{x}})+\kappa\nabla y(t,{\bm{x}})\cdot\nabla\varphi({\bm{x}})+\big({\bm{v}}({\bm{x}})\cdot\nabla y(t,{\bm{x}})\big)\varphi({\bm{x}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}-\kappa\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\partial y}{\partial{\bm{n}}}(t,{\bm{s}})\varphi({\bm{s}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{s}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}y_{t}(t,{\bm{x}})\varphi({\bm{x}})+\kappa\nabla y(t,{\bm{x}})\cdot\nabla\varphi({\bm{x}})+\big({\bm{v}}({\bm{x}})\cdot\nabla y(t,{\bm{x}})\big)\varphi({\bm{x}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle+\int_{\Gamma}q({\bm{s}})y(t,{\bm{s}})\varphi({\bm{s}})~\mathrm{d}{\bm{s}}-\int_{\Gamma}g(t,{\bm{s}})\varphi({\bm{s}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{s}}-\sum_{j=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}}u^{\mathsf{b}}_{j}(t)\int_{\Gamma}\xi_{j}({\bm{s}})\varphi({\bm{s}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{s}}

for almost all
t\in(0,T]
. We define the bilinear form
a:V\times V\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle a(\phi,\varphi)=\kappa\int_{\Omega}\nabla\phi({\bm{x}})\cdot\nabla\varphi({\bm{x}})+\big(v({\bm{x}})\cdot\nabla\phi({\bm{x}})\big)\varphi({\bm{x}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}+\int_{\Gamma}q({\bm{s}})\phi({\bm{s}})\varphi({\bm{s}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{s}}\quad\text{for }\phi,\varphi\in V.

We prove that there exists two constant
\gamma_{1}>0
and
\gamma_{2}\geq 0
such that

a(\varphi,\varphi)\geq\gamma_{1}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}^{2}-\gamma_{2}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{H}^{2}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in V.

(3.1.2)

For that purpose we take an arbitrary
\varphi\in V
, set
v_{\infty}=\|{\bm{v}}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{n}})}
and obtain

\displaystyle a(\varphi,\varphi)

\displaystyle\geq\kappa\int_{\Omega}{|\nabla\varphi({\bm{x}})|}_{2}^{2}+\big({\bm{v}}({\bm{x}})\cdot\nabla\varphi({\bm{x}})\big)\varphi({\bm{x}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\geq\kappa\big({\|\varphi\|}_{V}^{2}-{\|\varphi\|}_{H}^{2}\big)-v_{\infty}\,{\|\nabla\varphi\|}_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{n}})}{\|\varphi\|}_{H},

where the Bochner norms are recalled in Definition
A.4.55
. We continue to apply Young’s inequality (cf. Lemma
A.1.30
) to arrive at

\displaystyle a(\varphi,\varphi)

\displaystyle\geq\kappa\big({\|\varphi\|}_{V}^{2}-{\|\varphi\|}_{H}^{2}\big)-v_{\infty}\,\bigg(\frac{\kappa}{2v_{\infty}}\,{\|\nabla\varphi\|}_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{n}})}^{2}+\frac{v_{\infty}}{2\kappa}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{H}^{2}\bigg)\geq\gamma_{1}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}^{2}-\gamma_{2}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{H}^{2}

with
\gamma_{1}=\kappa/2>0
and
\gamma_{2}=(\kappa^{2}+v_{\infty}^{2})/(2\kappa)
. Therefore, the bilinear form
a(\cdot\,,\cdot)
satisfies (
3.1.2
). Furthermore, we obtain with
q_{\infty}=\|q\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\big|a(\phi,\varphi)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\kappa\int_{\Omega}\big|\nabla\phi({\bm{x}})\cdot\nabla\varphi({\bm{x}})\big|+\big|\big({\bm{v}}({\bm{x}})\cdot\nabla\phi({\bm{x}})\big)\varphi({\bm{x}})\big|\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}+q_{\infty}\int_{\Gamma}\big|\phi({\bm{s}})\varphi({\bm{s}})\big|\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{s}}

\displaystyle\leq\kappa\,{\|\phi\|}_{V}{\|\varphi\|}_{V}+v_{\infty}\,{\|\phi\|}_{V}{\|\varphi\|}_{V}+q_{\infty}\,{\|\phi\|}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}{\|\varphi\|}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}

for
\phi,\varphi\in V
. By the
trace theorem
(cf.
[
Eva08
]
) we have

{\|\varphi\|}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq\gamma_{\Gamma}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in V.

(3.1.3)

Thus, setting
\gamma=\kappa+\|b\|_{\infty}+\gamma_{\Gamma}^{2}\|q\|_{\infty}>0
, we have

\displaystyle\big|a(\phi,\varphi)\big|\leq\gamma\,{\|\phi\|}_{V}{\|\varphi\|}_{V}\quad\text{for all }\phi,\varphi\in V

i.e., the bilinear form
a(\cdot\,,\cdot)
is also
bounded
.

For
t\in(0,T)
the right-hand side
\mathcal{F}(t):V\to\mathbb{R}
be defined as

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{F}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}=\int_{\Omega}f(t,{\bm{x}})\varphi({\bm{x}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}+\int_{\Gamma}g(t,{\bm{s}})\varphi({\bm{s}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{s}}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in V.

For almost all
t\in(0,T)
and all
\varphi\in V
we derive that

\displaystyle\big|{\langle\mathcal{F}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\big|\leq{\|f(t,\cdot)\|}_{H}{\|\varphi\|}_{H}+\gamma_{\Gamma}\,{\|g(t,\cdot)\|}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}{\|\varphi\|}_{V}.

Since
f
and
g
are assumed to be essentially bounded and
\Omega
,
\Gamma
have finite measure, the functions
f
and
g
are square integrable. The above inequality implies that it holds
\mathcal{F}\in L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})
with

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{F}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}\leq{\|f\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}+\gamma_{\Gamma}\,{\|g\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Gamma))}.

Lastly, we also introduce for
u=(u^{\mathsf{d}},u^{\mathsf{b}})\in{\mathscr{U}}
and
t\in(0,T)
the linear
control operator

\displaystyle{\langle(\mathcal{B}u)(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}}u_{i}^{\mathsf{d}}(t)\int_{\Omega}\chi_{i}({\bm{x}})\varphi({\bm{x}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}+\sum_{j=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}}u_{i}^{\mathsf{b}}(t)\int_{\Gamma}\xi({\bm{s}})\varphi({\bm{s}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{s}}

for all
\varphi\in V
. Clearly, the linear operator
\mathcal{B}:\mathscr{U}\to L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})
is linear. We prove that
\mathcal{B}
is even continuous. For
t\in(0,T)
and
\varphi\in V
we get by applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and using (
2.4.1
) the estimate

\displaystyle\big|{\langle(\mathcal{B}u)(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\big|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}}|u^{\mathsf{d}}_{i}(t)|\int_{\Omega}\big|\chi_{i}({\bm{x}})\varphi({\bm{x}})\big|\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}+\sum_{j=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}}|u^{\mathsf{b}}_{j}(t)|\int_{\Gamma}\big|\xi_{j}({\bm{s}})\varphi({\bm{s}})\big|\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{s}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m_{\mathsf{d}}}|u^{\mathsf{d}}_{i}(t)|{\|\chi_{i}\|}_{H}{\|\varphi\|}_{H}+\sum_{j=1}^{m_{\mathsf{b}}}|u^{\mathsf{b}}_{j}(t)|{\|\xi_{j}\|}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}{\|\varphi\|}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq{\|\varphi\|}_{H}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}|u^{\mathsf{d}}_{i}(t)|{\|\chi_{i}\|}_{H}+{\|\varphi\|}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\sum_{j=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}}|u^{\mathsf{b}}_{j}(t)|{\|\xi_{j}\|}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}c_{V}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}{\|u^{\mathsf{d}}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{m_{\mathsf{d}}})}+c_{2}c_{\Gamma}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}{\|u^{\mathsf{b}}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{m_{\mathsf{b}}})}

\displaystyle\leq c\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}\sqrt{2}\big({\|u^{\mathsf{d}}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{m_{\mathsf{d}}})}^{2}+{\|u^{\mathsf{b}}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{m_{\mathsf{b}}})}^{2}\big)^{1/2}=\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}\,{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}{\|\varphi\|}_{V}

with
\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}=c\sqrt{2}
,
c=\max(c_{1},c_{V},c_{2}c_{\Gamma})
and

\displaystyle c_{1}=\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}{\|\chi_{i}\|}_{H}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2},\quad c_{2}=\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}}{\|\xi_{j}\|}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}

This proves in particular that the linear operator
\mathcal{B}
is bounded with

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{B}u\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}\leq\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}\,{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\quad\text{for all }u\in{\mathscr{U}}.

Finally, the weak form of the evolution problem (
3.1.1
) can be expressed in the form

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle y(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+a(y(t),\varphi)={\langle(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\text{ for }\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in }(0,T]

(3.1.4a)

together with the initial condition

{\langle y(0),\varphi\rangle}_{H}={\langle y_{\circ},\varphi\rangle}_{H}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in H.

(3.1.4b)

A solution
y
to (
3.1.4
) is called a
weak
or
variational solution
to (
3.1.1
).

Recall the linear space

\displaystyle W(0,T)=\big\{\varphi\in L^{2}(0,T;V)\,\big|\,\varphi_{t}\in L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})\big\}=L^{2}(0,T;V)\cap H^{1}(0,T;V^{\prime})

which is a Hilbert space endowed with the inner product

\displaystyle{\langle\varphi,\phi\rangle}_{W(0,T)}=\int_{0}^{T}{\langle\varphi_{t}(t),\phi_{t}(t)\rangle}_{V^{\prime}}+{\langle\varphi(t),\phi(t)\rangle}_{V}\,\mathrm{d}t\quad\text{for all }\varphi,\phi\in W(0,T)

and the induced norm
\|\cdot\|_{W(0,T)}=\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle_{W(0,T)}
. For more details we refer to Section
A.4
in the appendix. In particular, we have
H^{1,1}(Q)\hookrightarrow W(0,T)
. Due to Lemma
A.4.59
-4) we have for
y\in W(0,T)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle y(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}={\langle y_{t}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in V.

Thus, a solution to (
3.1.4
) do not need to be in
H^{1,1}(Q)
.

3.2
The abstract linear evolution problem

In this section, we introduce the abstract linear evolution problem which is mainly considered in this book. We discuss the unique solvability and finally present an a-priori error estimate for its solutions.

3.2.1
Problem formulation

We make use of the following hypotheses.
