Assumption 3.2.1
.

Suppose that
T>0
holds.

1)

V
and
H
are real, separable Hilbert spaces and
V
is dense in
H
with compact embedding. By
\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle_{H}
and
\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle_{V}
we denote the inner products in
H
and
V
, respectively. We identify
H
with its dual (Hilbert) space
H^{\prime}
by the Riesz isomorphism so that we have the
Gelfand triple

\displaystyle V\hookrightarrow H\simeq H^{\prime}\hookrightarrow V^{\prime},

where each embedding is continous and dense. The last embedding is understood as follows: For every element
h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
and
v\in V
, we also have
v\in H
by the embedding
V\hookrightarrow H
, so we can define
\langle h^{\prime},v\rangle_{V^{\prime},V}=\langle h^{\prime},v\rangle_{H^{\prime},H}
.

2)

The
control space
{\mathscr{U}}
is a real Hilbert space with inner product
\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle_{\mathscr{U}}
and induced norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{U}}=(\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle_{\mathscr{U}})^{1/2}
.

3)

For almost all
t\in[0,T]
we define a
time-dependent bilinear form
a(t;\cdot\,,\cdot):V\times V\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\displaystyle\big|a(t;\varphi,\psi)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}{\|\psi\|}_{V}

\displaystyle\forall\varphi,\psi\in V\text{ a.e. in }[0,T],

(3.2.1a)

\displaystyle a(t;\varphi,\varphi)

\displaystyle\geq\gamma_{1}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}^{2}-\gamma_{2}\,\|\varphi\|_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\forall\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in }[0,T],

(3.2.1b)

for constants
\gamma,\gamma_{2}\geq 0
and
\gamma_{1}>0
which do not depend on
t
.

4)

Assume that
y_{\circ}\in H
,
\mathcal{F}\in L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})
and that
\mathcal{B}:{\mathscr{U}}\to L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})
is a continuous, linear
control operator.

Remark 3.2.2
.

1)

We associate with
a(t;\cdot\,,\cdot)
a time-dependent linear and bounded operator
\mathcal{A}(t):V\to V^{\prime}
given by

{\langle\mathcal{A}(t)\varphi,\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}=a(t;\varphi,\psi)\quad\text{for }\varphi,\psi\in V\text{ and }t\in[0,T].

(3.2.2)

If the bilinear form
a
is time-independent, i.e.,
a:V\times V\to\mathbb{R}
, then the linear, bounded operator
\mathcal{A}
introduced in (
3.2.2
) is time-independent as well. In this case we define the
domain of
\mathcal{A}
as the linear subspace

D(\mathcal{A})=\big\{\varphi\in V\,\big|\,\mathcal{A}\varphi\in H\big\}.

(3.2.3)

It follows that

\displaystyle D(\mathcal{A})\hookrightarrow V\hookrightarrow H=H^{\prime}\hookrightarrow V^{\prime}

holds, each embedding being continuous and dense, when
\mathcal{A}
is bounded and
D(\mathcal{A})
is endowed with the
graph norm of
\mathcal{A}
, given by

\displaystyle{\|\varphi\|}_{\mathcal{A}}=\big({\|\varphi\|}_{V}^{2}+{\|\mathcal{A}\varphi\|}_{H}^{2}\big)^{1/2}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in D(\mathcal{A}).

2)

From Assumption
3.2.1
-1) it follows that there exists an embedding constant
c_{V}>0
satisfying (
2.4.1
).
\blacksquare

Let
{\mathscr{Y}}=W(0,T)
denote the
state space
; cf. (
A.4.5
). For fixed control
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
the state
y\in{\mathscr{Y}}
is governed by the linear evolution problem

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle y(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;y(t),\varphi)

\displaystyle={\langle(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69054pt\forall\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

(3.2.4a)

\displaystyle{\langle y(0),\varphi\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle={\langle y_{\circ},\varphi\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69054pt\forall\varphi\in H.

(3.2.4b)

Remark 3.2.3
.

Utilizing the operator
\mathcal{A}(t)
introduced in Remark
3.2.2
-2) we can write (
3.2.4
) also as the following linear time-variant dynamical system

\displaystyle\dot{y}(t)+\mathcal{A}(t)y(t)

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(t)

\displaystyle\text{ in }V^{\prime}\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

\displaystyle y(0)

\displaystyle=y_{\circ}

\displaystyle\text{ in }H

with the notation
\dot{y}=y_{t}
.
\blacksquare

3.2.2
Unique solvability and a-priori bound

Existence of a unique solution and an a-priori estimate for the solution is stated in the following theorem which is proved in Section
3.8.1
.

Theorem 3.2.4
.

Let Assumption
3.2.1
hold. Then for every
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
there is a unique weak solution
y\in{\mathscr{Y}}
satisfying (
3.2.4
) and

{\|y\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq C\left({\|y_{\circ}\|}_{H}+{\|\mathcal{F}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}+{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\right)

(3.2.5)

for a constant
C>0
which is independent of
u
,
y_{\circ}
and
f
. If
\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u\in L^{2}(0,T;H)
,
a(t;\cdot\,,\cdot)=a(\cdot\,,\cdot)
(i.e.,
a
is independent of
t
) and
y_{\circ}\in V
hold, we even have
y\in C([0,T];V)\cap H^{1}(0,T;H)\cap L^{2}(0,T;D(\mathcal{A}))
.

The following corollary follows directly from Theorem
3.2.4
.

Corollary 3.2.5
.

Let Assumption
3.2.1
hold.

1)

There exists a unique solution
\hat{y}\in{\mathscr{Y}}
to

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle\hat{y}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\hat{y}(t),\varphi)

\displaystyle={\langle\mathcal{F}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

\displaystyle\text{for all }\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

(3.2.6)

\displaystyle{\langle\hat{y}(0),\varphi\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle={\langle y_{\circ},\varphi\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle\text{for all }\varphi\in H

satisfying

\displaystyle{\|\hat{y}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq C\left({\|y_{\circ}\|}_{H}+{\|\mathcal{F}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}\right)

for a constant
C>0
.

2)

Let us define the linear
solution operator
\mathcal{S}:{\mathscr{U}}\to{\mathscr{Y}}
as follows: for every
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
the function
y=\mathcal{S}u
is the solution to

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle y(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;y(t),\varphi)

\displaystyle={\langle(\mathcal{B}u)(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

\displaystyle\text{for all }\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

(3.2.7)

\displaystyle{\langle y(0),\varphi\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{for all }\varphi\in H.

Then
\mathcal{S}
is well-defined and bounded, i.e.,

{\|\mathcal{S}u\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq C\,{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}

(3.2.8)

for a constant
C>0
which is independent of
u
.

Remark 3.2.6
.

We can express the solution to (
3.2.4
) as
y=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u
. The operator
\mathcal{S}
is often called
control-to-state operator
or
solution operator
. Notice that the (particular) solution
\hat{y}
does not depend on the control input
u
.
\blacksquare

It was already shown in Section
3.1
that the weak formulation of the recurring parabolic example satisfies Assumption
3.2.1
. This yields the following corollary.

Corollary 3.2.7
.

The recurring parabolic partial differential equation (
1.2.1
) in its weak formulation (
3.1.4
) admits a unique solution
y\in W(0,T)
which can be written as
y=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u
, where
\hat{y}\in W(0,T)
is the solution to (
3.2.6
) and
\mathcal{S}:{\mathscr{U}}\to W(0,T)
is introduced in Corollary
3.2.5
.

Example 3.2.8
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{n}}
,
\mathfrak{n}\in\{1,2,3\}
, be a bounded domain with Lipschitz-continuous boundary
\Gamma=\partial\Omega
. We set
H=L^{2}(\Omega)
and
V=H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
. The space
V
is endowed with inner product

\displaystyle{\langle\varphi,\phi\rangle}_{V}=\int_{\Omega}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\phi\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\quad\text{for }\varphi,\phi\in V

and the induced norm
\|\cdot\|_{V}=\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle_{V}^{1/2}
. If the time-independent bilinear form
a(\cdot\,,\cdot)
is given by

\displaystyle a(\varphi,\phi)={\langle\varphi,\phi\rangle}_{V}\quad\text{for }\varphi,\phi\in V,

\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u\in L^{2}(0,T;H
holds for the given
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
and
y_{\circ}\in V
is valid. it is shown in
[
DL00
, pp. 532-533]
that the solution
y=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u
belongs to the space
W(0,T)\cap L^{2}(0,T;H^{2}(\Omega))
. Since
H^{2}(\Omega)
is compactly embedded in
V\hookrightarrow H
, we infer from Aubin’s lemma
[
Tem79
, p. 271]
that
W(0,T)\cap L^{2}(0,T;H^{2}(\Omega))
is compactly embedded in
L^{2}(0,T;V)
.
\blacklozenge

3.3
The continuous POD method

In this section we apply a POD Galerkin approximation to the linear evolution problem (
3.2.4
), but no discretization of the temporal variable. Therefore, we utilize the
continuous variant of the POD method
introduced in Section
2.4.1
, where we distinguish two choices for
X
:
X=H
and
X=V
. The parameter domain
\mathscr{D}
is chosen to be the time interval
[0,T]
. Using the rate of convergence results presented in Section
2.4.1
, an a-priori error analysis is carried out for the difference between the (exact) solution to (
3.2.4
) and the one computed by the POD Galerkin scheme.

3.3.1
POD Galerkin scheme

We will utilize the following hypotheses.

Assumption 3.3.1
.

1)

Assumption
3.2.1
holds.

2)

The Hilbert space
X
denotes either
H
or
V
. For the choice
X=V
we additionally assume
y_{\circ}\in V
.

For given
\mathcal{F}\in L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})
,
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
and
y_{\circ}\in X
let
y\in{\mathscr{Y}}
be the unique solution to (
3.2.4
). In the context of Section
2.4.1
we choose
K=1
,
\omega_{1}^{K}=1
und
y^{1}=y\in L^{2}(0,T;X)
. Suppose that we have computed non-negative eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
and associated eigenfunctions
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
satisfying

\mathcal{R}\psi_{i}=\lambda_{i}\psi_{i}\text{ for }i\in\mathbb{I},\quad\left\{\begin{aligned} &\lambda_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{d}>\lambda_{d+1}=\ldots=0&&\text{if }d<\infty,\\
&\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\ldots\text{ and }\lim_{i\to\infty}\lambda_{i}=0&&\text{if }d=\infty.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.3.1)

If we want to indicate that the POD basis is computed by choosing
X=H
or
X=V
we write
\{\psi_{i}^{H}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
or
\{\psi_{i}^{V}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
, respectively. Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
be arbitrarily chosen with
\ell\leq d
provided
d
is finite. The integral operator
\mathcal{R}
has been introduced in (
2.2.2
). Then we define the POD subspace

\displaystyle X^{\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}\big\}\subset X.

From part 1) of Lemma
2.4.6
we infer that
X^{\ell}\subset V
holds true. If we want to consider either the specific case
X=H
or
X=V
we make use of the notation (cf. Section
2.4.1
)

\displaystyle H^{\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\left\{\psi_{1}^{H},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}^{H}\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad V^{\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\left\{\psi_{1}^{V},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}^{V}\right\},

The POD Galerkin scheme for (
3.2.4
) reads as follows: find
y^{\ell}:[0,T]\to X^{\ell}
satisfying

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle y^{\ell}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;y^{\ell}(t),\psi)

\displaystyle={\langle(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(t),\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\quad\text{for all }\psi\in X^{\ell}\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

(3.3.2a)

\displaystyle y^{\ell}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y_{\circ},

(3.3.2b)

where
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}:Z\to X^{\ell}
stands for one of the following four orthogonal projections (cf. (
2.4.6
)):

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}&&\text{for }X^{\ell}=H^{\ell}\text{ and }Z=H,\\
&\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}&&\text{for }X^{\ell}=H^{\ell}\text{ and }Z=V,\\
&\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}&&\text{for }X^{\ell}=V^{\ell}\text{ and }Z=H,\\
&\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{V}&&\text{for }X^{\ell}=V^{\ell}\text{ and }Z=V.\end{aligned}\right.

We can see that in comparison to the original reduced formulation (
3.2.4
), the space
V
has been replaced by the finite-dimensional linear space
X^{\ell}\subset V
. In the upcoming analysis, we will therefore be required to enhance the original Gelfand triple, which is done in the following remark.

Remark 3.3.2
.

Using the property of the
Gelfand triple
we get

\displaystyle V^{\ell}\hookrightarrow V\hookrightarrow H\simeq H^{\prime}\hookrightarrow V^{\prime}\hookrightarrow(V^{\ell})^{\prime}.

Here, for an arbitrary
\varphi\in V^{\ell}
the corresponding
\varphi\in V^{\prime}
is seen as the mapping

\displaystyle\psi\mapsto{\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle}_{H}={\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\quad\text{for all }\psi\in V.

The embedding
V^{\prime}\hookrightarrow(V^{\ell})^{\prime}
is done by restricting a function
\varphi\in V^{\prime}
onto the domain
V^{\ell}
. By doing so, it clearly holds

\displaystyle{\|\varphi\|}_{(V^{\ell})^{\prime}}\leq{\|\varphi\|}_{V^{\prime}}.

For an arbitrary
\varphi\in V^{\ell}
, the reverse additionally holds true. To see that, le
\psi\in V
with
{\|\psi\|}_{V}=1
be arbitrary. We can uniquely write
\psi
as
\psi=\psi^{\ell}+\psi^{\perp}
with
\psi^{\ell}\in V^{\ell}
and
\psi^{\perp}\in(V^{\ell})^{\perp_{H}}
, i.e.
\psi^{\perp}
is in the orthogonal complement of
V^{\ell}
with respect to the inner product of
H
. Then we get

\displaystyle{\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle_{V^{\prime},V}}={\langle\varphi,\psi^{\ell}+\psi^{\perp}\rangle_{H}}={\langle\varphi,\psi^{\ell}\rangle_{H}}={\langle\varphi,\psi^{\ell}\rangle_{V^{\prime},V}}\leq{\|\varphi\|}_{(V^{\ell})^{\prime}}.

Hence,

\displaystyle{\|\varphi\|}_{(V^{\ell})^{\prime}}={\|\varphi\|}_{V^{\prime}}\qquad\text{for all }\varphi\in V^{\ell}

(3.3.3)

holds true.
\blacksquare

It follows from
y^{\ell}(t)\in X^{\ell}
almost everywhere in
[0,T]
that there exists a vector
\mathrm{y}^{\ell}(t)=(\mathrm{y}_{i}^{\ell}(t))\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
(
t\in[0,T]
), which satisfies

y^{\ell}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{y}_{i}^{\ell}(t)\psi_{i}\quad\text{f.a.a. }t\in[0,T].

(3.3.4)

Let us define the
mass
and (time-dependent)
stiffness matrices

{\bm{M}}^{\ell}=\big(\big({\langle\psi_{j},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{H}\big)\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell\times\ell}\quad\text{and}\quad{\bm{A}}^{\ell}(t)=\big(\big(a(t;\psi_{j},\psi_{i})\big)\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell\times\ell},

(3.3.5)

respectively. Since the bilinear form
a(t;\cdot\,,\cdot)
might not be symmetric,
{\bm{A}}^{\ell}(t)
can not be assumed to be symmetric. The next result is shown in Section
3.8.2
.

Lemma 3.3.3
.

The mass matrix
{\bm{M}}^{\ell}
is symmetric and positive definite. For the stiffness matrix
{\bm{A}}^{\ell}(t)
we have

\mathrm{v}^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{\ell}(t)\mathrm{v}>0\quad\text{for all }\mathrm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\setminus\{0\}

(3.3.6)

provided (
3.2.1b
) is satisfied with
\gamma_{2}=0
. In particular,
{\bm{A}}^{\ell}(t)
is then regular in
[0,T]
.

Remark 3.3.4
.

Since the mass matrix
{\bm{M}}^{\ell}
is positive definite, we can introduce the weighted inner product

\displaystyle{\langle\mathrm{v}^{\ell},\mathrm{w}^{\ell}\rangle}_{{\bm{M}}^{\ell}}=\big(\mathrm{v}^{\ell}\big)^{\top}{\bm{M}}^{\ell}\mathrm{w}^{\ell}\quad\text{for }\mathrm{v}^{\ell}=(\mathrm{v}_{i}^{\ell})^{\top},\mathrm{w}^{\ell}=(\mathrm{w}_{i}^{\ell})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}.

It follows that

\displaystyle{\langle v^{\ell},w^{\ell}\rangle}_{H}={\langle\mathrm{v}^{\ell},\mathrm{w}^{\ell}\rangle}_{{\bm{M}}^{\ell}}\quad\text{for }v^{\ell}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{v}_{i}^{\ell}\psi_{i},\,w^{\ell}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{w}_{i}^{\ell}\psi_{i}

which implies also
\|v^{\ell}\|_{H}=\|\mathrm{v}\|_{{\bm{M}}^{\ell}}
. Especially, the POD subspace
H^{\ell}
is isometrically isomorph to the space
\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
of the POD coefficients endowed with the weighted inner product
\langle v^{\ell},w^{\ell}\rangle_{{\bm{M}}^{\ell}}=(v^{\ell})^{\top}{\bm{M}}^{\ell}w^{\ell}
. Likewise,
V^{\ell}
is isometrically isomorph to the space
\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
endowed with the weighted inner product
\langle v^{\ell},w^{\ell}\rangle_{{\bm{S}}^{\ell}}=(v^{\ell})^{\top}{\bm{S}}^{\ell}w^{\ell}
with the matrix
{\bm{S}}^{\ell}=((S_{ij}^{\ell}))\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell\times\ell}
given by
{\bm{S}}_{ij}^{\ell}=\langle\psi_{j},\psi_{i}\rangle_{V}
for
i,j=1,...,\ell
; cf. Example
2.1.25
-b).
\blacksquare

Inserting (
3.3.4
) into (
3.3.2a
) and choosing
\psi=\psi_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq\ell
we derive the following system of ordinary differential equations for
\mathrm{y}^{\ell}
:

{\bm{M}}^{\ell}\dot{\mathrm{y}}^{\ell}(t)+{\bm{A}}^{\ell}(t)\mathrm{y}^{\ell}(t)=\mathrm{g}^{\ell}(t;u)\quad\text{f.a.a. }t\in(0,T],

(3.3.7a)

where we have set

\displaystyle\mathrm{g}^{\ell}(t;u)=\left({\langle(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(t),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\quad\text{for }u\in{\mathscr{U}}\text{ and }t\in[0,T].

Since
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y_{\circ}\in X^{\ell}
holds, there is exactly one coefficient
\mathrm{y}_{\circ}^{\ell}=(\mathrm{y}_{\circ i}^{\ell})\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y_{\circ}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{y}_{\circ i}\psi_{i}.

Then (
3.3.2b
) reads

\mathrm{y}^{\ell}(0)=\mathrm{y}_{\circ}^{\ell}.

(3.3.7b)

If
\mathrm{y}^{\ell}
solves (
3.3.7
), then
y^{\ell}
given by (
3.3.4
) is a solution to (
3.3.2
). The other way round, the coefficient vector
\mathrm{y}^{\ell}
solves (
3.3.7
) provided
y^{\ell}
is a solution to (
3.3.2
).

Note that (
3.3.7
) is an
\ell
-dimensional initial value problem. Since the mapping
t\mapsto\mathrm{g}^{\ell}(t;u)
might not be continuous in
[0,T]
for given
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
, the existence of a unique solution
\mathrm{y}^{\ell}
to (
3.3.7
) does not follow from the theory of Picard-Lindelöf. However, existence of a unique solution can be ensured by arguing similarly as in the proof of Theorem
3.2.4
; cf. Section
3.8.2
.

Theorem 3.3.5
.

Let Assumption
3.3.1
hold and
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
solve (
3.3.1
) for all
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
. Then for every
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
, there is a unique weak solution
y^{\ell}\in H^{1}(0,T;V)\hookrightarrow{\mathscr{Y}}=W(0,T)
satisfying (
3.3.2
) and

{\|y^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq C\left({\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}+{\|\mathcal{F}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}+{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\right)

(3.3.8)

for a constant
C>0
which is independent of
\ell
,
u
,
y_{\circ}
and
\mathcal{F}
.

Remark 3.3.6
.

If
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}
or
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}
(i.e.,
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
is an
H
-orthogonal projection), then we have

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}\leq{\|y_{\circ}\|}_{H}.

If
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}
or
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{V}
is chosen (i.e.,
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
is an
H
-orthogonal projection), we conclude that
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y_{\circ}\in V
for any
y_{\circ}\in V
. Using the embedding inequality (
2.4.1
) and
\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\|_{\mathscr{L}(V)}=1
we derive

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}\leq c_{V}\,{\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y_{\circ}\|}_{V}\leq c_{V}\,{\|y_{\circ}\|}_{V}

for any initial condition satisfying
y_{\circ}\in V
. Consequently, if
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
is either an
H
- or a
V
-orthogonal projection, we infer from (
3.3.8
) that

\displaystyle{\|y^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq\left\{\begin{aligned} &C\left({\|y_{\circ}\|}_{H}+{\|\mathcal{F}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}+{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\right)&&\text{if }\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}\text{ or }\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H},\\
&\tilde{C}\left({\|y_{\circ}\|}_{V}+{\|\mathcal{F}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}+{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\right)&&\text{if }\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}\text{ or }\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{V}\\
\end{aligned}\right.

for
\tilde{C}=\max\{1,c_{V}\}\,C
. In particular,
y^{\ell}
is bounded in the
\mathscr{Y}
-norm by a constant that does not depend on
\ell
.
\blacksquare

Although the weak solution
y^{\ell}
to (
3.3.2
) belongs to
H^{1}(0,T;V^{\ell})
, we do not get an
H^{1}(0,T;V)
-estimate like (
3.3.8
) with an
\ell
-independent constant
C
. The reason is given in the next lemma which is proved in Section
3.8.2
.

Lemma 3.3.7
.

Let Assumption
3.2.1
-1) hold, but both
V
and
H
be of identical finite dimension
\mathfrak{n}<\infty
. Then the space
\mathscr{Y}
is isomorphic to the space
H^{1}(0,T;V)
. In particular, there exists a constant
C_{\mathfrak{n}}>0
such that the following inverse inequality holds:

\displaystyle{\|\varphi\|}_{H^{1}(0,T;V)}\leq C_{\mathfrak{n}}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in H^{1}(0,T;V).

(3.3.9)

Remark 3.3.8
.

It follows from Lemma
3.3.7
, (
3.3.8
) and Remark
3.3.6
that there exists a constant
C_{\ell}>0
depending on
\ell
so that

\displaystyle{\|y^{\ell}\|}_{H^{1}(0,T;V)}\leq C_{\ell}\left({\|y_{\circ}\|}_{H}+{\|\mathcal{F}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}+{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\right)

holds true.
\blacksquare

The following corollary is a consequence of Theorem
3.3.5
; cf. Corollary
3.2.5
.

Corollary 3.3.9
.

Let Assumption
3.3.1
hold.

1)

There is a unique solution
\hat{y}^{\ell}\in H^{1}(0,T;V)\hookrightarrow{\mathscr{Y}}
to

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle\hat{y}^{\ell}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\hat{y}^{\ell}(t),\psi)&={\langle\mathcal{F}(t),\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\quad\text{for all }\psi\in V^{\ell}\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],\\
\hat{y}^{\ell}(0)&=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y_{\circ}.\end{aligned}

(3.3.10)

Moreover,

\displaystyle{\|\hat{y}^{\ell}\|}_{{\mathscr{Y}}}\leq C\left({\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}+{\|\mathcal{F}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}\right)

for a constant
C>0
which is independent of
\ell
,
y_{\circ}
and
\mathcal{F}
.

2)

Let us define the linear
POD solution operator
\mathcal{S}^{\ell}:{\mathscr{U}}\to{\mathscr{Y}}
as follows: for every
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
the function
\tilde{y}^{\ell}=\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u
is the solution to

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle\tilde{y}^{\ell}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\tilde{y}^{\ell}(t),\psi)&={\langle(\mathcal{B}u)(t),\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\quad\text{for all }\psi\in V^{\ell}\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],\\
\tilde{y}^{\ell}(0)&=0.\end{aligned}

(3.3.11)

Then
\mathcal{S}^{\ell}
is well-defined and continuous, i.e.,

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq C\,{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}

holds for all
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
for a constant
C>0
which is independent of
\ell
and
u
. Additionally,
\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u\in H^{1}(0,T;V)
for all
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
.

Remark 3.3.10
.

Similar to Remark
3.2.6
we can express the solution to (
3.3.2
) as
y^{\ell}=\hat{y}^{\ell}+\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u
. Again, the linear operator
\mathcal{S}^{\ell}
is called a
linear control-to-state operator
.
\blacksquare

3.3.2
POD a-priori error analysis

Let Assumption
3.3.1
hold. Our next goal is to derive a-priori error estimates for the term

\displaystyle{\|y-y^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}=\int_{0}^{T}{\|y(t)-y^{\ell}(t)\|}^{2}_{V}+{\|y_{t}(t)-y_{t}^{\ell}(t)\|}^{2}_{V^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}t,

where
y
and
y^{\ell}
are the solutions to (
3.2.4
) and (
3.3.2
), respectively, and
\mathscr{Y}=W(0,T)
holds. We will later specify the snapshots which are utilized to compute the POD basis
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell
. Recall that we have introduced the projection
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}:Z\to X^{\ell}
in (
3.3.2b
). We shall make use of the following decomposition (cf., e.g.,
[
Tho06
, p. 8]
and
[
KV01
,
KV02b
,
KV02a
]
)

y(t)-y^{\ell}(t)=y(t)-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y(t)+\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y(t)-y^{\ell}(t)=\varrho^{\ell}(t)+\vartheta^{\ell}(t)\quad\text{in }[0,T]\text{ a.e.}

(3.3.12)

with
\varrho^{\ell}(t)=y(t)-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y(t)\in(X^{\ell})^{\bot}
and
\vartheta^{\ell}(t)=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y(t)-y^{\ell}(t)\in X^{\ell}
. It follows that

{\|y-y^{\ell}\|}^{2}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq 2\big({\|\varrho^{\ell}\|}^{2}_{\mathscr{Y}}+{\|\vartheta^{\ell}\|}^{2}_{\mathscr{Y}}\big).

(3.3.13)

First we estimate the
\vartheta^{\ell}
-term. For a proof we refer the reader to Section
3.8.2
.

Lemma 3.3.11
.

Let Assumption
3.3.1
be fulfilled. Additionally, suppose that
y\in\mathscr{Y}
holds. Then

{\|\vartheta^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}\leq C\,{\|\varrho^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}

(3.3.14)

for a constant
C>0
which are independent of
\ell
,
y_{\circ}
,
f
and
u
.

Remark 3.3.12
.

1)

Combining (
3.3.13
) and (
3.3.14
) we infer that

{\|y-y^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}\leq C_{1}\,{\|\varrho^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}.

(3.3.15)

for
C_{1}=2(1+C)>0
.

2)

Recall that
L^{2}(0,T;H)\hookrightarrow L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})
holds; cf.
[
DL00
, p. 471]
. Thus, there is an embedding constant
\hat{c}_{\mathsf{e}}>0
satisfying

{\|\varphi\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}\leq\hat{c}_{\mathsf{e}}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in L^{2}(0,T;H).

(3.3.16)

If the solution
y
to (
3.2.4
) belongs to
H^{1}(0,T;H)\cap L^{2}(0,T;V)\hookrightarrow W(0,T)
, we derive from (
3.3.15
) and (
3.3.16
) that

\displaystyle{\|y-y^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\,{\|\varrho^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}=C_{1}\big({\|\varrho^{\ell}_{t}\|}_{L(0,T;V^{\prime})}^{2}+{\|\varrho^{\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}\big)

(3.3.17)

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\big(\hat{c}_{\mathsf{e}}^{2}\,{\|\varrho^{\ell}_{t}\|}_{L(0,T;H)}^{2}+{\|\varrho^{\ell}\|}_{L(0,T;V)}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=C_{2}\big({\|\varrho^{\ell}_{t}\|}_{L(0,T;H)}^{2}+{\|\varrho^{\ell}\|}_{L(0,T;V)}^{2}\big)

holds true for
C_{2}=C_{1}\max\{1,\hat{c}_{\mathsf{e}}^{2}\}>0
.

3)

From the two embeddings
H^{1}(0,T;V)\hookrightarrow H^{1}(0,T;V^{\prime})
and
H^{1}(0,T;V)\hookrightarrow L^{2}(0,T;V)
(cf.
[
DL00
, p. 471]
) and from
W(0,T)=H^{1}(0,T;V^{\prime})\cap L^{2}(0,T;V)
we infer that
H^{1}(0,T;V)\hookrightarrow W(0,T)
. Then, there is a constant
\tilde{c}_{\mathsf{e}}>0
such that

{\|\varphi\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq\tilde{c}_{\mathsf{e}}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{H^{1}(0,T;V)}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in H^{1}(0,T;V).

(3.3.18)

Suppose that the solution
y
to (
3.2.4
) belongs to
H^{1}(0,T;V)
. Then, it follows from (
3.3.15
) and (
3.3.18
) that

{\|y-y^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}\leq C_{1}\,{\|\varrho^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}\leq C_{3}\,{\|\varrho^{\ell}\|}_{H^{1}(0,T;V)}^{2}

(3.3.19)

is satisfied for
C_{3}=\tilde{c}_{\mathsf{e}}^{2}C_{1}>0
.
\blacksquare

If
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
is an
H
-orthonormal projection (i.e.,
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}_{H}^{\ell}
or
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}_{H}^{\ell}
), it turns out that we can omit the time derivatives of
\varrho^{\ell}
in our estimate; see Section
3.8.2
for a proof.

Lemma 3.3.13
.

Let Assumption
3.3.1
be fulfilled. Additionally, suppose that
y\in\mathscr{Y}\cap H^{1}(0,T;H)
holds and that
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
denotes either
\mathcal{P}_{H}^{\ell}
or
\mathcal{Q}_{H}^{\ell}
. Set
\vartheta^{\ell}(t)=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y(t)-y^{\ell}(t)\in X^{\ell}
for all
t\in[0,T]
. Then

{\|\vartheta^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}\leq\tilde{C}\,{\|\varrho^{\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}

(3.3.20)

for a constant
\tilde{C}>0
which are independent of
\ell
,
y_{\circ}
,
\mathcal{F}
and
u
. In particular, no time derivatives of
\varrho^{\ell}
occur on the right-hand side of (
3.3.20
).

Next, we discuss the choices
X=H
and
V
as well as different choices for the orthogonal projection
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
. For that purpose we proceed as in Section
2.4.1
and introduce different notations for the POD bases in
H
and
V
: Let the eigenvalue-eigenfunction pairs
\{(\lambda_{i}^{H},\psi_{i}^{H})\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
and
\{(\lambda_{i}^{V},\psi_{i}^{V})\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
satisfy

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{H}\psi_{i}^{H}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{i}^{H}\psi_{i}^{H},

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\lambda_{1}^{H}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{d}^{H}>\lambda_{d+1}^{H}=\ldots=0&&\text{if }d=d^{H}<\infty,\\
&\lambda_{1}^{H}\geq\lambda_{2}^{H}\geq\ldots\text{ and }\lim_{i\to\infty}\lambda_{i}^{H}=0&&\text{if }d^{H}=\infty,\end{aligned}\right.

(3.3.21a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{V}\psi_{i}^{V}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{i}^{V}\psi_{i}^{V},

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\lambda_{1}^{H}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{d}^{V}>\lambda_{d+1}^{V}=\ldots=0&&\text{if }d=d^{V}<\infty,\\
&\lambda_{1}^{H}\geq\lambda_{2}^{V}\geq\ldots\text{ and }\lim_{i\to\infty}\lambda_{i}^{V}=0&&\text{if }d^{V}=\infty,\end{aligned}\right.

(3.3.21b)

where the integral operators
\mathcal{R}^{H}
and
\mathcal{R}^{V}
have been introduced in (
2.4.2
). Next we analyze different choices for the snapshots in the POD method.

Snapshot space with time derivatives

Let us particularly assume that
y\in H^{1}(0,T;X)
holds and that we have set
K=2
,
\omega_{1}^{K}=\omega_{2}^{K}=1
,
y^{1}=y
and
y^{2}=y_{t}
, so both the solution trajectory and its derivative are considered to build the POD basis. Then we distinguish the following choices for the orthogonal projection
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
(cf. (
2.4.6
))

1)

Case
X=H
and
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}
:
In this case we have
y\in H^{1}(0,T;H)
so that we can apply Remark
3.3.12
-2). We infer for all
\ell\geq 1
from (
3.3.17
), (
2.4.10b
) and (
2.4.9a
)

\displaystyle{\|y-y^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\int_{0}^{T}\big\|y^{1}(t)-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}y^{1}(t)\big\|_{V}^{2}+\big\|y^{2}(t)-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}y^{2}(t)\big\|_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\big\|y^{k}(t)-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}y^{k}(t)\big\|_{V}^{2}+\big\|y^{k}(t)-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}y^{k}(t)\big\|_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=C_{2}\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{H}\big({\|\psi_{i}^{H}\|}_{V}^{2}+1\big).

2)

Case
X=H
and
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}
:
Again we have
y\in H^{1}(0,T;H)
and can utilze Remark
3.3.12
-2). From (
3.3.17
), (
2.4.1
) and (
2.4.10c
) we find for all
\ell\geq 1

\displaystyle{\|y-y^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\int_{0}^{T}\big\|y^{1}(t)-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}y^{1}(t)\big\|_{V}^{2}+\big\|y^{2}(t)-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}y^{2}(t)\big\|_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\int_{0}^{T}\big\|y^{1}(t)-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}y^{1}(t)\big\|_{V}^{2}+c_{V}^{2}\big\|y^{2}(t)-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}y^{2}(t)\big\|_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{4}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\big\|y^{k}(t)-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}y^{k}(t)\big\|_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t=C_{4}\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{H}\big\|\psi_{i}^{H}-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}\psi_{i}^{H}\big\|_{V}^{2}.

with
C_{4}=\max\{1,c_{V}^{2}\}C_{2}
.

3)

Case
X=V
and
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}
:
Now we have
y\in H^{1}(0,T;V)
so that Remark
3.3.12
-3) can be used. We combine (
3.3.19
) and (
2.4.10d
). It follows for all
\ell\geq 1
that

\displaystyle{\|y-y^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\big\|y^{k}(t)-\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}y^{k}(t)\big\|_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=C_{3}\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{V}{\|\psi_{i}^{V}-\mathcal{Q}_{H}^{\ell}\psi_{i}^{V}\|}_{V}^{2}.

4)

Case
X=V
and
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{V}
:
Due to (
3.3.19
) and (
2.4.10e
) and we derive that

\displaystyle{\|y-y^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}\leq C_{3}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\big\|y^{k}(t)-\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{V}y^{k}(t)\big\|_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t=C_{3}\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{V}.

Notice that we have in all four cases an estimate of the form

\displaystyle{\|y-y^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}\leq C_{5}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}{\|y^{k}-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y^{k}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}

for the constant
C_{5}=\max\{2C_{2},C_{3},C_{4}\}>0
. Using
[
Sin14
, Theorems 5.2 and 5.3]
we get convergence of
y-y^{\ell}
in
\mathscr{Y}
, where we have to suppose in the case
X=H
and
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}
that
\|\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}\|_{\mathscr{L}(V)}
is bounded independently of
\ell
. Summarizing we have derived the following theorem.

Theorem 3.3.14
.

Let Assumption
3.3.1
hold. Assume that for
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
and
y_{\circ}\in X
the unique solution
y
to (
3.2.4
) satisfies
y\in H^{1}(0,T;X)
. In (

P
ℓ

H

) and (

P
ℓ

V

) we choose
K=2
,
\omega_{1}^{K}=\omega_{2}^{K}=1
,
y^{1}=y
and
y^{2}=y_{t}
. Let the eigenvalue-eigenfunction pairs
\{(\lambda_{i}^{H},\psi_{i}^{H})\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
and
\{(\lambda_{i}^{V},\psi_{i}^{V})\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
satisfy (
3.3.21
). Furthermore,
y^{\ell}
denotes the solution to (
3.3.2
). Then we have for all
\ell\geq 1
the following a-priori error estimate

\displaystyle{\|y-y^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}\leq C\cdot\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{H}\big({\|\psi_{i}^{H}\|}_{V}^{2}+1\big)&&\text{for }X=H,~\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H},\\
&\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{H}\big\|\psi_{i}^{H}-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}\psi_{i}^{H}\big\|_{V}^{2}&&\text{for }X=H,~\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V},\\
&\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{V}{\|\psi_{i}^{V}-\mathcal{Q}_{H}^{\ell}\psi_{i}^{V}\|}_{V}^{2}&&\text{for }X=V,~\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H},\\
&\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{V}&&\text{for }X=V,~\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{V}\end{aligned}\right.

for a constant
C>0
that is independent of
\ell
,
y_{\circ}
,
f
and
u
. Moreover, we have
\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\|y-y^{\ell}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}=0
, where we have to suppose in the case
X=H
and
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}
that
\|\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}\|_{\mathscr{L}(V)}
is bounded independently of
\ell
.

Remark 3.3.15
.

1)

For the choices
X=V
and
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{V}
the error decays as the POD aproximation error; cf. (
2.4.9b
). Moreover, the evalution of

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}y_{\circ}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{\circ},\psi_{i}^{V}\rangle}_{V}\,\psi_{i}^{V}

is computationally cheap. The only issue is the computation of the inner products
\langle y_{\circ},\psi_{i}^{V}\rangle_{V}
for
i=1,\ldots,\ell
. However, we require that the snapshots
y
and
y_{t}
are in
L^{2}(0,T,V)
, so that the solution to (
3.2.4
) has to belong to
H^{1}(0,T;V)
which can not be expected at all; cf. Theorem
3.2.4
. Less regularity of the solution – in fact,
y\in H^{1}(0,T;H)
– is needed for the choices
X=H
and
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}
. Here, the effort to compute

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}y_{\circ}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{\circ},\psi_{i}^{H}\rangle}_{H}\,\psi_{i}^{H}

is just like the computation of the projection
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}y_{\circ}
. However, the decay rate for the a-priori error is damped by the
V
-norm of the basis elements
\psi_{i}^{H}
. A better decay is acchievd for the choices
X=H
and
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{VH^{\ell}}
, but now the evaluation of
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{VH^{\ell}}y_{\circ}
requires an
\ell
-dimensional system solve and, in addition, the computation of the inner products
\langle y_{\circ},\psi_{i}^{V}\rangle_{V}
for
i=1,\ldots,\ell
.

2)

Clearly, we can also bound the difference
y-y^{\ell}
in the
L^{2}(0,T;H)
norm by utilizing (
2.4.1
) in

\displaystyle{\|y-y^{\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}^{2}\leq c_{V}^{2}\,{\|y-y^{\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}^{2}\leq c_{V}^{2}\,{\|y-y^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}

and applying Theorem
3.3.14
.
\blacksquare

Snapshot space with arbitrary data trajectory

Next we discuss the situation when the POD basis is computed from snapshots which are different from the solution
y
to (
3.2.4
). We choose
\mathscr{D}=[0,T]
and arbitrary trajectories
\{y^{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}\subset L^{2}(0,T;X)
in (
2.2.3
). Furthermore, the solution
y
to (
3.2.4
) belongs to
H^{1}(0,T;X)\hookrightarrow\mathscr{Y}
holds so that we can apply Remark
3.3.12
-2). Due to (
3.3.17
), we still have

\displaystyle{\|y-y^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}\leq C_{2}\int_{0}^{T}\big\|y(t)-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y(t)\big\|_{V}^{2}+\,\big\|y_{t}(t)-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y_{t}(t)\big\|_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

Since the solution
y
is not utilized to get the POD basis of rank
\ell
, we can not apply Theorem
2.4.13
in order to estimate the right-hand side (as we have done to prove Theorem
2.4.13
). However, Proposition
2.4.10
can be used. Let
X=H
and
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}
. If
\lambda_{i}^{H}>0
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
we get

\displaystyle\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\left({\|y(t)-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}y(t)\|}_{V}^{2}+{\|y_{t}(t)-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}y_{t}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}\right)=0\quad\text{in }[0,T]\text{ a.e.}

provided
y(t)\in\mathrm{ran}\,(\mathcal{Y}_{H})
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{H}\phi=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\phi^{k}(t)y^{k}(t)\,\mathrm{d}0\quad\text{for }\phi=(\phi^{1},\ldots,\phi^{K})\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{k}).

Next we consider again
X=H
, but
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}
. Again, we have to assume
\lambda_{i}^{H}>0
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
in order to apply Proposition
2.4.10
. Using (
2.4.1
) we find

\displaystyle\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\left({\|y(t)-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}y(t)\|}_{V}^{2}+{\|y_{t}(t)-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}y_{t}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\left({\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}y(t)-y(t)\|}_{V}^{2}+c_{V}^{2}\,{\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}y_{t}(t)-y_{t}(t)\|}_{V}^{2}\right)=0\quad\text{in }[0,T]\text{ a.e.}

for
y(t),\,y_{t}(t)\in V
provided
\lambda_{i}^{H}>0
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Finally, applying (
2.4.1
) we find

\displaystyle\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\left({\|\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{\ell}y(t)-y(t)\|}_{V}^{2}+{\|\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{\ell}y_{t}(t)-y_{t}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\left({\|\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{\ell}y(t)-y(t)\|}_{V}^{2}+c_{V}^{2}\,{\|\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{\ell}y_{t}(t)-y_{t}(t)\|}_{V}^{2}\right)=0\quad\text{in }[0,T]\text{ a.e.}

for
y(t),\,y_{t}(t)\in V
. Consequently, we have the following result:

Theorem 3.3.16
.

Suppose that Assumption
3.3.1
holds. For
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
and
y_{\circ}\in X
let the unique solution
y
to (
3.2.4
) belong to
H^{1}(0,T;X)
. The POD basis of rank
\ell
be computed by solving (

P
ℓ

H

) or (

P
ℓ

V

) for an arbitrarily chosen snapshot ensemble
\{y^{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}\subset L^{2}(0,T;V)
, where we assume, in addition, that

1)

\lambda_{i}^{H}>0
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
y\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{ran}\,(\mathcal{Y}_{H}))
for
X=H
and
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}
,

2)

\lambda_{i}^{H}>0
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
y\in H^{1}(0,T;V)
for
X=H
and
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}
.

Let
y^{\ell}
denote the solution to (
3.3.2
). Then we have
\lim_{\ell\to\infty}{\|y-y^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}=0
.

Snapshot space without time derivatives

We infer from Theorem
3.3.14
the rate of convergence for the difference
y-y^{\ell}
in the
L^{2}(0,T;V)
norm provided
y
and
y_{t}
are utilized as snapshots to compute the POD bases
\{\psi_{i}^{H}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
and
\{\psi_{i}^{V}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
. For a specific setting we can avoid to include the time derivatives into the snapshot ensemble.

Notice that

\displaystyle{\|\varphi\|}^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}\leq{\|\varphi\|}^{2}_{\mathscr{Y}}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in\mathscr{Y}

Moreover, recall that
\varrho^{\ell}=y-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y
and
\vartheta^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y-y^{\ell}
. Due to Lemma
3.3.13
we have

{\|y-y^{\ell}\|}^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}\leq 2\big({\|\varrho^{\ell}\|}^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}+{\|\vartheta^{\ell}\|}^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}\big)\leq\tilde{C}_{1}\,{\|\varrho^{\ell}\|}^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}

(3.3.22)

with the constant
\tilde{C}_{1}=2(1+\tilde{C})
. Now the next theorem follows by using Theorem
2.4.13
.

Theorem 3.3.17
.

Let Assumption
3.3.1
hold. Assume that for
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
the unique solution
y
to (
3.2.4
) satisfies
y\in H^{1}(0,T;H)
and that
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
is given either by
\mathcal{P}_{H}^{\ell}
or
\mathcal{Q}_{H}^{\ell}
. In (

P
ℓ

H

) and (

P
ℓ

V

) we choose
K=1
,
\omega_{1}^{K}=1
and
y^{1}=y
. Let the eigenvalue-eigenfunction pairs
\{(\lambda_{i}^{H},\psi_{i}^{H})\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
and
\{(\lambda_{i}^{V},\psi_{i}^{V})\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
satisfy (
3.3.21
). Furthermore,
y^{\ell}
denotes the solution to (
3.3.2
). Then there exists a constant
C>0
satisfying the following a-priori error estimate

\displaystyle{\|y-y^{\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}\leq C\cdot\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{H}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{H}\big\|_{V}^{2},&&X=H,~\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H},\\
&\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{V}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{V}-\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}\psi_{i}^{V}\big\|_{V}^{2},&&X=V,~\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}.\end{aligned}\right.

Operator convergence

Recall that we have introduced the linear, bounded operators
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\ell}
in Corollaries
3.2.5
-2 and
3.3.9
-2, respectively. Moreover, the linear projection operator
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
has been utilized in (
3.3.2b
). We give sufficient conditions that
\mathcal{S}^{\ell}
converges to
\mathcal{S}
in
\mathscr{L}({\mathscr{U}},{\mathscr{Y}})
as
\ell\to\infty
. The proof of the next result is given in Section
3.8.2
.

Theorem 3.3.18
.

Let Assumption
3.3.1
hold. We suppose that
{\mathscr{U}}
is a finite-dimensional Hilbert space. Moreover, let one of the following hypotheses be satisfied:

1)

X=H
,
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}
,
\lambda_{i}^{H}>0
for all
i\in\mathbb{I}
and
y(t)
,
y_{t}(t)\in\mathrm{ran}\,(\mathcal{Y})\subset V
for almost all
t\in[0,T]
;

2)

X=H
,
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{VH^{\ell}}
,
\lambda_{i}^{H}>0
holds for all
i\in\mathbb{I}
;

3)

Case
X=V
,
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}
:
\lambda_{i}^{V}>0
for all
i\in\mathbb{I}
.

Then
\|\mathcal{S}^{\ell}-\mathcal{S}\|_{\mathscr{L}({\mathscr{U}},{\mathscr{Y}})}\to 0
as
\ell\to\infty
.

Remark 3.3.19
.

To prove the statement of Theorem
3.3.18
in the case that
{\mathscr{U}}
is an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space we additionally need that the operator
\mathcal{S}
is compact and that the operator family
\{\mathcal{S}^{\ell}\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly compact in the sense that

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u^{\ell}-\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u_{\circ}\|}_{{\mathscr{Y}}}\to 0\quad\text{as }\ell\to\infty

holds for all sequences
\{u^{\ell}\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\subset{\mathscr{U}}
with
u^{\ell}\rightharpoonup u_{\circ}
as
\ell\to\infty
.
\blacksquare

3.4
The semidiscrete approximation

To compute the POD basis
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
as described in Section
3.3
, we need the snapshots
y(t)
for almost all
t\in[0,T]
. This is realized numerically by computing approximations for
y(t)
using a spatial and temporal discretization method. First, we consider a Galerkin discretization method in which the snapshots are given by a finite linear combination of basis functions but the time variable is still kept continuous. In a second step we turn to the temporal discretization. We refer the reader to
[
Tho06
]
, where Galerkin discretizations for evolution problems are considered.

3.4.1
Galerkin discretization

For a given mesh size parameter
h>0
and associated number of nodes
m=m(h)\in\mathbb{N}
let
\{\varphi_{i}^{h}\}_{i=1}^{m}\subset V
be linearly independent functions. Then we introduce the
m
-dimensional subspace

V^{h}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{m}\big\}\subset V

(3.4.1)

endowed with the topology in
V
. We make use of the following hypotheses.

Assumption 3.4.1
.

There exists a subspace
\mathscr{W}\hookrightarrow V
which is dense in
V
, such that

\inf_{v^{h}\in V^{h}}\big\{{\|v-v^{h}\|}_{H}+h\,{\|v-v^{h}\|}_{V}\big\}\leq C_{\mathscr{W}}\,h^{2}\,{\|v\|}_{\mathscr{W}}\quad\text{for any }v\in\mathscr{W},

(3.4.2)

where the constant
C_{\mathscr{W}}
is independent of
h
and
v
.

Example 3.4.2
.

Suppose that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a bounded, convex polygonal domain with boundary
\Gamma=\partial\Omega
. Let
\{\mathcal{T}^{h}\}_{h>0}
denote a family of partitions of
\Omega
into disjunct triangles such that no vertex of any triangle lies on the interior of a side of another triangle and such that the union of the closures of all triangles is equal to
\overline{\Omega}
; cf. Figure
3.4.1
.

Figure 3.4.1
:
Example
3.4.2
. Finite element triangulation of the unit square
\Omega=(0,1)^{2}
using
m=41
vertices and a maximal triangle side length of
h=0.2
.

Let
h>0
denote the maximal length of the sides of the triangulation
\mathcal{T}^{h}
. Thus, if
h
decreases,
\mathcal{T}^{h}
is made finer. Let the angles of the triangulation be bounded from below by a positive constant, independently of
h
. If we choose piecewise linear
finite elements (FE) functions

\{\varphi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
as a basis for
V^{h}
, we get (
3.4.2
) for
\mathscr{W}=H^{2}(\Omega)\cap V
; cf.
[
BS08
, pp. 105-110]
.
\blacklozenge

Remark 3.4.3
.

Estimate (
3.4.2
) is often ensured by assuming the existence of a family of projection or interpolation operators
\{\mathcal{P}^{h}\}_{h>0}
with
\mathcal{P}^{h}:\mathscr{W}\to V^{h}
satisfying

{\|v-\mathcal{P}^{h}v\|}_{H}+h\,{\|v-\mathcal{P}^{h}v\|}_{V}\leq\hat{C}_{\mathscr{W}}\,h^{2}\,{\|v\|}_{\mathscr{W}}\quad\text{for any }v\in\mathscr{W},

(3.4.3)

where the constant
\hat{C}_{\mathscr{W}}
is independent of
h
and
v
.
\blacksquare

We apply a standard Galerkin scheme for (
3.2.4
). Thus, we look for a function
y^{h}
satisfying
y^{h}(t)\in V^{h}
in
[0,T]
almost everywhere and

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle y^{h}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;y^{h}(t),\varphi)

\displaystyle={\langle(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in V^{h}\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

(3.4.4a)

\displaystyle y^{h}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{h}y_{\circ},

(3.4.4b)

where
\mathcal{P}^{h}:X\to V^{h}
is an appropriate linear projection operator for the respective choice
X=V
or
X=H
.

Example 3.4.4
.

Let us present two possible choices for the projection operator
\mathcal{P}^{h}
.

1)

For any
w\in H
the element
v^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}w\in V^{h}
has to satisfy

\displaystyle{\langle v^{h},\varphi_{i}^{h}\rangle}_{H}={\langle w,\varphi_{i}^{h}\rangle}_{H}\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,m.

Utilizing the representation
v^{h}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathrm{v}_{j}^{h}\varphi_{j}^{h}\in V^{h}
the coefficient vector
\mathrm{v}^{h}=(\mathrm{v}_{i}^{h})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
is uniquely determined as the solution to the linear system

{\bm{M}}^{h}\mathrm{v}^{h}=\mathrm{w}^{h}

(3.4.5)

with the
mass matrix

{\bm{M}}^{h}=(({\langle\varphi_{j}^{h},\varphi_{i}^{h}\rangle}_{H}))\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
and the right-hand side
\mathrm{w}^{h}=(\langle w,\varphi_{i}^{h}\rangle_{H})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. From (
3.4.5
) we infer that

{\langle\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}y_{\circ},\varphi_{i}^{h}\rangle}_{H}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathrm{v}_{j}^{h}\,{\langle\varphi_{j}^{h},\varphi_{i}^{h}\rangle}_{H}={\langle y_{\circ},\varphi_{i}^{h}\rangle}_{H}

(3.4.6)

holds for
i=1,\ldots,m
. Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}w\|}_{H}^{2}={\langle\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}w,\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}w\rangle}_{H}={\langle w,\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}w\rangle}_{H}\leq{\|w\|}_{H}{\|\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}w\|}_{H},

i.e.,
\|\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}w\|_{H}\leq\|w\|_{H}
for every
w\in H
. Moreover,
\|\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}w^{h}\|_{H}=\|w^{h}\|_{H}
for every
w^{h}\in V^{h}\subset H
. This implies

{\|\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}\|}_{\mathscr{L}(H)}=1.

(3.4.7)

From
\|\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}w\|_{H}\leq\|w\|_{H}
for every
w\in H
and (
2.4.1
) we also infer that

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}w\|}_{H}\leq c_{V}\,{\|w\|}_{V}\quad\text{for every }w\in V

which implies that
\|\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}\|_{\mathscr{L}(V,H)}\leq c_{V}
.

2)

We also introduce a linear projection
\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}\in L(V,V^{h})
as follows: For any
w\in V
the element
v^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}w\in V^{h}
is given as

\displaystyle{\langle v^{h},\varphi_{i}^{h}\rangle}_{V}={\langle w,\varphi_{i}^{h}\rangle}_{V}\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,m.

From
v^{h}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathrm{v}_{j}^{h}\varphi_{j}^{h}\in V^{h}
the coefficient vector
\mathrm{v}^{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
is the unique solution to the linear system

{\bm{S}}^{h}\mathrm{v}^{h}=\mathrm{w}^{h}

(3.4.8)

with the symmetric, positive definite
stiffness matrix

{\bm{S}}^{h}=(({\langle\varphi_{j}^{h},\varphi_{i}^{h}\rangle}_{V}))\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
and the right-hand side
\mathrm{w}^{h}=(\langle w,\varphi_{i}^{h}\rangle_{V})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Utilizing (
3.4.8
) we find that

{\langle\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}y_{\circ},\varphi_{i}^{h}\rangle}_{V}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathrm{v}_{j}^{h}\,{\langle\varphi_{j}^{h},\varphi_{i}^{h}\rangle}_{V}={\langle y_{\circ},\varphi_{i}^{h}\rangle}_{V}

(3.4.9)

holds for
i=1,\ldots,m
. Analogously to part 1) we derive that

{\|\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}\|}_{\mathscr{L}(V)}=1.

(3.4.10)

Applying (
3.4.10
) and the embedding inequality (
2.4.1
) we also conclude that

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}\|}_{\mathscr{L}(V,H)}

\displaystyle=\sup_{{\|w\|}_{V}=1}{\|\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}w\|}_{H}\leq c_{V}\sup_{{\|w\|}_{V}=1}{\|\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}w\|}_{V}\leq c_{V}\,{\|\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}\|}_{\mathscr{L}(V)}=c_{V}

(3.4.11)

holds true.
\blacklozenge

Since
y^{h}(t)\in V^{h}
is assumed, we have the representation

y^{h}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{y}^{h}_{i}(t)\varphi_{i}^{h}\in V^{h}

(3.4.12)

with a modal coefficient vector

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}^{h}(t)=\big(\mathrm{y}_{i}^{h}(t)\big)_{1\leq i\leq m}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\quad\text{for }t\in[0,T].

From (
3.4.4
) and (
3.4.12
) we derive the linear system of ordinary differential equations

{\bm{M}}^{h}\dot{\mathrm{y}}^{h}(t)+{\bm{A}}^{h}(t)\mathrm{y}^{h}(t)=\mathrm{g}^{h}(t;u)\text{ f.a.a. }t\in(0,T],\quad{\bm{M}}^{h}\mathrm{y}^{h}(0)=\mathrm{y}_{\circ}^{h}

(3.4.13)

with the mass matrix
{\bm{M}}^{h}
introduced in Example
3.4.4
, the
stiffness matrix

{\bm{A}}^{h}(t)=\big(\big(a(t;\varphi_{j}^{h},\varphi_{i}^{h})\big)\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}\quad\text{f.a.a. }t\in[0,T]

(3.4.14)

and the vectors

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}_{\circ}^{h}=\big({\langle y_{\circ},\varphi_{i}^{h}\rangle}_{H}\big)_{1\leq i\leq m},\,\mathrm{g}^{h}(t;u)=\big({\langle(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(t),\varphi_{i}^{h}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\big)_{1\leq i\leq m}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}

for almost all
t\in[0,T]
and all
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
. The next result follows by similar arguments used in the proof of Theorem
3.3.5
.

Theorem 3.4.5
.

Let Assumption
3.2.1
hold. Suppose that
\{\varphi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
are linearly independent functions in
V
and that
V^{h}
is given by (
3.4.1
). Then for every
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
there is a unique weak solution
y^{h}\in H^{1}(0,T;V)\hookrightarrow{\mathscr{Y}}
satisfying (
3.4.4
) and the a-priori bound

{\|y^{h}\|}_{{\mathscr{Y}}}\leq C\left({\|\mathcal{P}^{h}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}+{\|\mathcal{F}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}+{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\right)

(3.4.15)

for a constant
C>0
which is independent of
u
,
y_{\circ}
and
f
.

Remark 3.4.6
.

In Example
3.4.4
we have introduced the projection
\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}
. Then it follows from

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}^{2}={\langle\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}y_{\circ},\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}y_{\circ}\rangle}_{H}={\langle y_{\circ},\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}y_{\circ}\rangle}_{H}\leq{\|y_{\circ}\|}_{H}{\|\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}

that
\|\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}y_{\circ}\|_{H}\leq\|y_{\circ}\|_{H}
holds so that (
3.4.15
) can be replaced by

\displaystyle{\|y^{h}\|}_{{\mathscr{Y}}}\leq C\left({\|y_{\circ}\|}_{H}+{\|\mathcal{F}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}+{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\right)

where the right-hand side is independent of
h
. In the case of
\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}
and
y_{\circ}\in V
we find that
\|\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}y_{\circ}\|_{H}\leq c_{V}\,\|y_{\circ}\|_{V}
, where the constant
c_{V}
is given by (
2.4.1
). Thus, we infer from (
3.4.15
) that

\displaystyle{\|y^{h}\|}_{{\mathscr{Y}}}\leq\tilde{C}\left({\|y_{\circ}\|}_{H}+{\|\mathcal{F}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}+{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\right)

holds with
\tilde{C}=C\max(1,c_{V})
.
\blacksquare

3.4.2
A-priori error analysis

Now we estimate the error between the true solution
y
of (
3.2.4
) and the semidiscrete solution
y^{h}
of (
3.4.4
). For the proof we refer to Section
3.8.3
.

Theorem 3.4.7
.

Let Assumptions
3.2.1
and
3.4.1
hold. We suppose that
y
and
y^{h}
are the solutions to (
3.2.4
) and (
3.4.4
), respectively. Moreover, assume that
y\in H^{1}(0,T;\mathscr{W})
. Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that the following a-priori error estimates hold:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}{\|y^{h}(t)-y(t)\|}_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{2}\left(h^{2}\,{\|y\|}_{H^{1}(0,T;\mathscr{W})}^{2}+{\|y\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathscr{W})}^{2}\right),

(3.4.16)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}{\|y^{h}(t)-y(t)\|}_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{2}\left(h^{2}\,{\|y_{t}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathscr{W})}^{2}+{\|y\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathscr{W})}^{2}\right).

3.4.3
POD basis computation

This subsection focusses on the POD basis computation utilizing the semidiscrete solution
y^{h}\in H^{1}(0,T;V^{h})
and its derivative as data vectors. Suppose that Assumption
3.3.1
is satisfied. By

y^{h}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{y}_{i}^{h}(t)\varphi_{i}\quad\text{for all }t\in[0,T],

(3.4.17)

we denote the solution to (
3.4.4
). In the context of Section
2.4.1
we choose
K=2
,
\omega_{1}^{K}=\omega_{2}^{K}=1
,
y^{1}=y^{h}
and
y^{2}=y_{t}^{h}
. From Theorem
3.4.5
we conclude that
y^{h}\in H^{1}(0,T;V^{h})
. Thus,
y^{1},\,y^{2}\in L^{2}(0,T;X)
for
X=H
and

\mathcal{R}\psi_{i}=\lambda_{i}\psi_{i}\text{ for }i\in\mathbb{I},\quad\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{d}>\lambda_{d+1}=\ldots=0

(3.4.18)

where the finite-rank operator
\mathcal{R}:X\to V^{h}\subset X
is given as

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\psi=\int_{0}^{T}\Big({\langle y^{h}(t),\psi\rangle}_{X}\,y^{h}(t)+{\langle y^{h}_{t}(t),\psi\rangle}_{X}\,y^{h}_{t}(t)\Big)\,\mathrm{d}t\quad\text{for }\psi\in X

and
d=\dim\big\{\mathcal{R}\psi\,\big|\,\psi\in X\big\}\leq m
.

Let us mention that
\mathcal{R}
and therefore
\{(\lambda_{i},\psi_{i})\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
as well as
d
depend on
h
To simplify the notation we so not indicate this dependence by a superscript
h
.

Since
d
is finite,
\mathcal{R}
is a finite rank operator. It follows from Lemma
2.4.6
-2) that
d
is the same for the choices
X=H
and
X=V
. Then
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset V^{h}
is a POD basis of rank
\ell
solving

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\min\int_{0}^{T}\Big\|y^{h}(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y^{h}(t),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{X}^{2}+\Big\|y_{t}^{h}(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{t}^{h}(t),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{X}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 2.84526pt\text{s.t. }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset X\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{X}=\delta_{ij}\text{ for }1\leq i,j\leq\ell.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.4.19)

For
\{(\lambda_{i},\psi_{i})\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
satisfying (
3.4.18
) we obtain for every
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d\}

\int_{0}^{T}\Big\|y^{h}(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y^{h}(t),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{X}^{2}+\Big\|y_{t}^{h}(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{t}^{h}(t),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{X}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d}\lambda_{i}.

(3.4.20)

From
\psi_{i}=\mathcal{R}\psi_{i}/\lambda_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,\ell\leq d^{h}
, we infer that there exists a unique coefficient matrix
{\bm{\Psi}}=[\uppsi_{1}\,|\ldots\,\uppsi_{\ell}]\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times\ell}
with the elements
\Psi_{ij}=(\uppsi_{j})_{i}
and columns
{\bm{\Psi}}_{\cdot\,,j}=\uppsi_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that

\psi_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\Psi_{ji}\varphi_{j}\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,\ell.

(3.4.21)

Let us introduce the positive definite, symmetric
weighting matrix

\displaystyle{\bm{W}}=((\langle\varphi_{j},\varphi_{i}\rangle_{X}))\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}

and the associated weighted inner product

\displaystyle{\langle\mathrm{v},\mathrm{w}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}=\mathrm{v}^{\top}{\bm{W}}\mathrm{w}\quad\text{for }\mathrm{v},\,\mathrm{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.

with the induced norm
|\cdot|_{\bm{W}}={\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle}_{\bm{W}}^{1/2}
. Then we conclude from (
3.4.17
) and (
3.4.21
) that

\displaystyle{\langle y^{h}(t),\psi_{j}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\langle\mathrm{y}^{h}(t),\uppsi_{i}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}\,\Psi_{ij}\varphi_{i}\quad\text{for }j=1,\ldots,\ell.

Hence, we find

\displaystyle\Big\|y^{h}(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y^{h}(t),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{X}^{2}=\Big|\mathrm{y}^{h}(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle\mathrm{y}^{h}(t),\uppsi_{i}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}\,\uppsi_{i}\Big|_{\bm{W}}^{2}

and

\displaystyle\Big\|y_{t}^{h}(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y^{h}_{t}(t),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{X}^{2}=\Big|\dot{\mathrm{y}}^{h}(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle\dot{\mathrm{y}}^{h}(t),\uppsi_{i}\rangle}_{{\bm{W}}}\,\uppsi_{i}\Big|_{\bm{W}}^{2}

Summarizing, (
3.4.19
) is equivalent to the minimization problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\min\int_{0}^{T}\Big|\mathrm{y}^{h}(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle\mathrm{y}^{h}(t),\uppsi_{i}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}\,\uppsi_{i}\Big|_{\bm{W}}^{2}+\Big|\dot{\mathrm{y}}^{h}(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle\dot{\mathrm{y}}^{h}(t),\uppsi_{i}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}\,\uppsi_{i}\Big|_{\bm{W}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 2.84526pt\text{s.t. }\{\uppsi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\text{ and }{\langle\uppsi_{i},\uppsi_{j}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}=\delta_{ij}\text{ for }1\leq i,j\leq\ell.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.4.22)

If
\{\uppsi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
solves (
3.4.22
) then
\{\psi_{i}^{h}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
is a solution to (
3.4.19
) with

\displaystyle\psi_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\big(\uppsi_{i}\big)_{j}\varphi_{i}\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,\ell.

On the other hand, if
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
is a solution to (
3.4.19
) then we infer from
\psi_{i}\in V^{h}
the existence of a uniquely determined matrix
{\bm{\Psi}}=[\uppsi_{1}\,|\ldots|\,\uppsi_{\ell}]\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times\ell}
satisfying (
3.4.21
). It follows that the columns
\{\uppsi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of
{\bm{\Psi}}
solve (
3.4.22
).

3.4.4
POD Galerkin scheme

After having computed a POD basis, this subsection illurstrates the Galerkin discretization of the semidiscrete system onto the subspace spanned by this POD basis. Suppose therefore that we have computed a POD basis
\{\psi_{i}^{h}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell
with
\ell\leq d^{h}\leq m
. Let Assumption
3.3.1
be satisfied. We set

\displaystyle X^{h\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}\big\}\subset X\subset V^{h}

and

\displaystyle H^{h\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\psi_{1}^{H},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}^{H}\big\},\quad V^{h\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\psi_{1}^{V},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}^{V}\big\}

for the choices
X=H
and
X=V
, respectively. For the reader’s convenience we omit the indication of the dependence of the eigenvectors
\{\psi_{i}^{H}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
and
\{\psi_{i}^{V}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
on the spatial discretization by an additional superscript
h
. Then we replace (
3.4.4
) by the following POD Galerkin scheme: for given
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
find
y^{h\ell}:[0,T]\to X^{h\ell}
satisfying

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle y^{h\ell}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;y^{h\ell}(t),\psi)

\displaystyle={\langle(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(t),\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

(3.4.23a)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\forall\psi\in X^{h\ell}\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

\displaystyle y^{h\ell}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}y_{\circ},

(3.4.23b)

where
\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}:Z\to X^{h\ell}
stands for one of the following four orthogonal projections (cf. (
2.4.6
)):

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}_{H}&&\text{for }X=H^{h\ell}\text{ and }Z=H,\\
&\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}_{V}&&\text{for }X=H^{h\ell}\text{ and }Z=V,\\
&\mathcal{Q}^{h\ell}_{H}&&\text{for }X=V^{h\ell}\text{ and }Z=H,\\
&\mathcal{Q}^{h\ell}_{V}&&\text{for }X=V^{h\ell}\text{ and }Z=V.\end{aligned}\right.

From
y^{h\ell}(t)\in X^{h\ell}
it follows that there exists a coefficient vector

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}^{h\ell}(t)=\big(\mathrm{y}_{i}^{h\ell}(t)\big)_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\quad\text{a.e. in }[0,T]

satisfying

y^{h\ell}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{y}_{i}^{h\ell}(t)\psi_{i}\quad\text{a.e. in }[0,T].

(3.4.24)

Inserting (
3.4.24
) into (
3.4.23a
) and using
\psi=\psi_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq\ell
, we derive the following linear system of ordinary differential equations

{\bm{M}}^{h\ell}\dot{\mathrm{y}}^{h\ell}(t)+{\bm{A}}^{h\ell}(t)\mathrm{y}^{h\ell}(t)=\mathrm{g}^{h\ell}(t;u)\quad\text{a.e. in }(0,T]

(3.4.25a)

with the matrices

\displaystyle{\bm{M}}^{h\ell}=\big(\big({\langle\psi_{j},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{H}\big)\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell\times\ell},\quad{\bm{A}}^{h\ell}(t)=\big(\big(a(t;\psi_{j},\psi_{i})\big)\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell\times\ell}

and the vector

\displaystyle\mathrm{g}^{h\ell}(t;u)=\big({\langle(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(t),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\big)_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\quad\text{for }u\in{\mathscr{U}}\text{ and a.e. in }[0,T].

From (
3.4.23b
) we infer that

\mathrm{y}^{h\ell}(0)=\mathrm{y}_{\circ}^{h\ell}=\big(\mathrm{y}_{\circ i}^{h\ell}\big)_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\quad\text{with}\quad\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}y_{\circ}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{y}_{\circ i}^{h\ell}\psi_{i}.

(3.4.25b)

Arguing as in the proof of Theorem
3.3.5
we obtain the next result.

Theorem 3.4.8
.

Suppose that Assumption
3.3.1
holds. Moreover, assume that
\ell\leq d_{h}
holds. Then for any
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
there exists a unique weak solution
y^{h\ell}\in H^{1}(0,T;V)
satisfying (
3.4.23
). In particular, we have the a-priori bound

\displaystyle{\|y^{h\ell}\|}_{{\mathscr{Y}}}\leq C\left({\|\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}+{\|\mathcal{F}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}+{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\right)

for a constant
C>0
which is independent of
\ell
,
y_{\circ}
,
\mathcal{F}
and
u
.

The next result is a direct consequence of Theorem
3.4.8
; Corollaries
3.2.5
and
3.3.9
.

Corollary 3.4.9
.

Suppose that Assumption
3.3.1
holds. Then we have for every
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
:

1)

There exists a unique solution
\hat{y}^{h\ell}
satisfying
\hat{y}^{h\ell}(t)\in X^{\ell}
in
[0,T]
almost everywhere and

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle\hat{y}^{h\ell}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\hat{y}^{h\ell}(t),\psi)

\displaystyle={\langle\mathcal{F}(t),\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\quad\text{for all }\psi\in X^{\ell}\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

\displaystyle\hat{y}^{h\ell}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}y_{\circ}.

Moreover, there exists a constant
C\geq 0
which is independent of
\ell
,
y_{\circ}
and
\mathcal{F}
with the a-priori bound

\displaystyle{\|\hat{y}^{h\ell}\|}_{{\mathscr{Y}}}\leq C\big({\|\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}+{\|\mathcal{F}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}\big).

2)

Let us define the linear operator
\mathcal{S}^{h\ell}:{\mathscr{U}}\to{\mathscr{Y}}
as follows:
y^{h\ell}=\mathcal{S}^{h\ell}u
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle y^{h\ell}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;y^{h\ell}(t),\psi)

\displaystyle={\langle(\mathcal{B}u)(t),\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\quad\text{for all }\psi^{h}\in X^{h\ell}\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

\displaystyle y^{h\ell}(0)

\displaystyle=0.

Then
\mathcal{S}^{h\ell}
is well-defined and bounded, i.e.,

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{S}^{h\ell}u\|}_{{\mathscr{Y}}}\leq C\,{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}

for a constant
C\geq 0
which is independent of
\ell
and
u
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{S}^{h\ell}u\in H^{1}(0,T;V)
holds for all
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
.

Remark 3.4.10
.

Let us mention that the solution to (
3.4.23
) can be expressed as
y^{h\ell}=\hat{y}^{h\ell}+\mathcal{S}^{h\ell}u
.
\blacksquare

3.4.5
POD a-priori error analysis

As in Section
3.3.2
we are interested to estimate the error between the solutions
y
and
y^{h\ell}
to (
3.2.4
) and (
3.4.23
), respectively. Here we make use of the triangle inequality and Theorem
3.4.7
, where we already present an a-priori error estimate for the semidiscrete solution
y^{h}
to (
3.4.4
). Hence, if Assumptions
3.2.1
and
3.4.1
hold we find that

\displaystyle{\|y-y^{h\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\,{\|y-y^{h}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}+2\,{\|y^{h}-y^{h\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}=\mathcal{O}\big(h^{2}\big)+2\,{\|y^{h}-y^{h\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}.

Thus, our goal is to derive an a-priori error estimate for the term

\displaystyle{\|y^{h}-y^{h\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}=\int_{0}^{T}{\|y^{h}(t)-y^{h\ell}(t)\|}_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t.

We can follow the analysis carried out in Section
3.3.2
, where we directly apply the error formulas of Section
2.4.1
. Let us we make use of the following decomposition (compare (
3.3.12
))

\displaystyle y^{h}(t)-y^{h\ell}(t)=y^{h}(t)-\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}y^{h}(t)+\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}y^{h}(t)-y^{h\ell}(t)=\varrho^{h\ell}(t)+\vartheta^{h\ell}(t)\text{ a.e. in }[0,T]

with
\varrho^{h\ell}(t)=y^{h}(t)-\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}y^{h}(t)\in(X^{h\ell})^{\bot}
and
\vartheta^{h\ell}(t)=\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}y^{h}(t)-y^{h\ell}(t)\in X^{h\ell}
. To achieve that

\displaystyle\vartheta^{h\ell}(0)=\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}y^{h}(0)-y^{h\ell}(0)=\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}\big(\mathcal{P}^{h}y_{\circ}\big)-\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}y_{\circ}=0

we choose the operators
\mathcal{P}^{h}
appropriately; cf. Example
3.4.4
.

Remark 3.4.11
.

1)

X=H
,
\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}_{H}
and
\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}
:
We infer from (
3.4.6
) and
\psi_{i}^{H}\in V^{h}
for
1\leq i\leq\ell
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}_{H}\big(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}y_{\circ}\big)=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}y_{\circ},\psi_{i}^{H}\rangle}_{H}\,\psi_{i}^{H}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{\circ},\psi_{i}^{H}\rangle}_{H}\,\psi_{i}^{H}=\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}_{H}y_{\circ}

which implies
\vartheta^{h\ell}(0)=0
.

2)

X=H
,
\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}_{V}
and
\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}
:
We suppose that
y_{\circ}\in V
holds. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}_{V}\big(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}y_{\circ}\big)=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{v}_{i}\psi_{i}^{H},\quad\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}_{V}y_{\circ}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{w}_{i}\psi_{i}^{H}

for coefficient vectors
\mathrm{v}=(\mathrm{v}_{i}),\,\mathrm{w}=(\mathrm{w}_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
. Utilizing
\psi_{i}^{H}\in V^{h}
for
1\leq i\leq\ell
, (
2.4.8a
), (
3.4.9
) and again (
2.4.8a
) we find

\displaystyle\big({\bm{S}}^{h\ell}\mathrm{v}\big)_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}{\langle\psi_{j}^{H},\psi_{i}^{H}\rangle}_{V}\mathrm{v}_{j}={\langle\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}_{V}(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}y_{\circ}),\psi_{i}^{H}\rangle}_{V}={\langle\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}y_{\circ},\psi_{i}^{H}\rangle}_{V}={\langle y_{\circ},\psi_{i}^{H}\rangle}_{V}

\displaystyle={\langle\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}_{V}y_{\circ},\psi_{i}^{H}\rangle}_{V}=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}{\langle\psi_{j}^{H},\psi_{i}^{H}\rangle}_{V}\mathrm{w}_{j}=\big({\bm{S}}^{h\ell}\mathrm{w}\big)_{i}\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,\ell,

where
{\bm{S}}^{h\ell}=((\langle\psi_{j}^{H},\psi_{i}^{H}\rangle_{V}))\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell\times\ell}
is invertible. This implies that
\mathrm{v}=\mathrm{w}
holds. Consequently,
\vartheta^{h\ell}(0)=0
.

3)

X=V
,
\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{h\ell}_{H}
and
\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}
:
We argue similar to case 2). There exist coefficient vectors
\mathrm{v}=(\mathrm{v}_{i}),\,\mathrm{w}=(\mathrm{w}_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{h\ell}_{H}\big(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}y_{\circ}\big)=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{v}_{i}\psi_{i}^{V},\quad\mathcal{Q}^{h\ell}_{H}y_{\circ}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{w}_{i}\psi_{i}^{V}

holds. From
\psi_{i}^{V}\in V^{h}
for
1\leq i\leq\ell
, (
2.4.8b
), (
3.4.6
) and again (
2.4.8b
) we derive

\displaystyle\big({\bm{M}}^{h\ell}\mathrm{v}\big)_{i}={\langle\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}y_{\circ},\psi_{i}^{V}\rangle}_{H}={\langle y_{\circ},\psi_{i}^{V}\rangle}_{H}=\big({\bm{M}}^{h\ell}\mathrm{w}\big)_{i}\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,\ell,

where
{\bm{M}}^{h\ell}=((\langle\psi_{j}^{V},\psi_{i}^{V}\rangle_{H}))\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell\times\ell}
is invertible. Again,
\mathrm{v}=\mathrm{w}
is satisfied which gives
\vartheta^{h\ell}(0)=0
.

4)

X=V
,
\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{h\ell}_{V}
and
\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}
:
It follows from Assumption
3.3.1
-3) that
y_{\circ}\in V
holds for
X=V
. Due to (
3.4.9
) we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{h\ell}_{V}\big(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}y_{\circ}\big)=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}y_{\circ},\psi_{i}^{V}\rangle}_{V}\,\psi_{i}^{V}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{\circ},\psi_{i}^{V}\rangle}_{V}\,\psi_{i}^{V}=\mathcal{Q}^{h\ell}_{V}y_{\circ}.

Again, we obtain
\vartheta^{h\ell}(0)=0
.
\blacksquare

Now we can argue as in the proof of Theorem
3.3.14
.

Theorem 3.4.12
.

Let Assumption
3.3.1
hold. We utilize the unique solution
y^{h}
to (
3.4.4
) to compute the POD basis by solving (
3.4.22
). Let the eigenvalue-eigenfunction pairs
\{(\lambda_{i}^{H},\psi_{i}^{H})\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
and
\{(\lambda_{i}^{V},\psi_{i}^{V})\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
satisfy (
3.4.18
) either for
X=H
or for
X=V
, respectively. Furthermore,
y^{h\ell}
stands for the solution to (
3.4.23
). Then we have for all
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
the following a-priori error estimate

\displaystyle{\|y^{h}-y^{h\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}\leq C\cdot\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{H}\big({\|\psi_{i}^{H}\|}_{V}^{2}+1\big),&&X=H,~\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H},~\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H},\\
&\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{H}\,{\|\psi_{i}^{H}-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}\psi_{i}^{H}\|}_{V}^{2},&&X=H,~\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}_{V},~\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V},~y_{\circ}\in V,\\
&\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{V}\,{\|\psi_{i}^{V}-\mathcal{Q}_{H}^{h\ell}\psi_{i}^{V}\|}_{V}^{2},&&X=V,~\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{h\ell}_{H},~\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H},\\
&\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{V},&&X=V,~\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{V},~\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}\end{aligned}\right.

for a constant
C>0
that is independent of
\ell
,
y_{\circ}
,
\mathcal{F}
and
u
.

Extensions

Next we consider the case where the time derivative
y_{t}^{h}\in L^{2}(0,T;V)
is not included in the snapshot set for the computation of the POD basis; cf. Section
3.3.2
. Then we determine the POD basis
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
from the minimization problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\min\int_{0}^{T}\Big\|y^{h}(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y^{h}(t),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}^{h}\Big\|_{X}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 2.84526pt\text{s.t. }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset X\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{X}=\delta_{ij}\text{ for }1\leq i,j\leq\ell.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.4.26)

The solution to (
3.4.26
) can be computed by solving

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\psi_{i}=\lambda_{i}\psi_{i}\text{ for }i\in\mathbb{I},\quad\lambda_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{d}>\lambda_{d+1}=\ldots=0

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\psi=\int_{0}^{T}{\langle y^{h}(t),\psi\rangle}_{X}\,y^{h}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\quad\text{for }\psi\in X

and
d=\dim\big\{\mathcal{R}\psi\,\big|\,\psi\in X\big\}\leq m
. Then it follows by the same arguments as in the proof of Theorem
3.3.17
that there exists a constant
C>0
that is independent of
\ell
,
y_{\circ}
,
f
and
u
satisfying the a-priori error estimates

\int_{0}^{T}{\|y^{h}(t)-y^{h\ell}(t)\|}_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\cdot\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{H}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{h}\big\|_{V}^{2},&&X=H,~\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H},~\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H},\\
&\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{V}\,{\|\psi_{i}^{V}-\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}\psi_{i}^{V}\|}_{V}^{2},&&X=V,~\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H},~\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V},\\
&&&y_{\circ}\in V.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.4.27)

To prove (
3.4.27
) we utilize that
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}
are
H
-orthonormal projections. This allows us to derive the property (
3.8.20
) for
y^{h}
. Let us mention that we can also derive bounds for the difference
y^{h}-y^{h\ell}
in the
{\mathscr{Y}}
-norm proceeding as in the end of Section
3.3.2
.

3.4.6
POD a-posteriori error analysis

In general, the a-priori error analysis presented in Theorem
3.4.12
can not be utilized directly in numerical realizations for two reasons:

\blacksquare

The rate of convergence results are only available for a given
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
if the corresponding finite element trajectories
y^{h}(t)
and
y^{h}_{t}(t)
are available. In this case, it is not meaningful to compute a POD solution
y^{h\ell}
in addition.

\blacksquare

The constants in Theorem
3.4.12
are often difficult to estimate accurately, so that the bounds may become inefficient.

For that reason, an a-posteriori error analysis is developed which allows us to estimate the error between the finite element and the POD solution for a given
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
. An essential point is that the computation of the POD basis does not require the solution
y^{h}(t)
. The proof of the next theorem is given in Section
3.8.3
.

Theorem 3.4.13
.

Suppose that Assumption
3.3.1
holds and that
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
. Let
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
be any POD basis of rank
\ell
. Let
y^{h}
and
y^{h\ell}
be the solutions to (
3.4.4
) and (
3.4.23
), respectively. We define the residual

\displaystyle r^{h\ell}(t;u)={\langle y^{h\ell}_{t}(t;u)-(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(t),\cdot\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}+a(t;y^{h\ell}(t;u),\cdot)\in(V^{h})^{\prime}

for almost all
t\in[0,T]
. Then the following a-posteriori error estimates hold

\displaystyle{\|y^{h}(t)-y^{h\ell}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}\leq e^{ct}\bigg({\|\mathcal{P}^{h}y_{\circ}-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\,{\|r^{h\ell}(\cdot\,;u)\|}_{L^{2}(0,t;(V^{h})^{\prime})}^{2}\bigg)

almost everywhere in
[0,T]
with
c=\max(2\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{1}/c_{V}^{2},0)
and

\displaystyle{\|y^{h}-y^{h\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}\leq\frac{1+2\gamma_{2}Te^{ct}}{\gamma_{1}}\bigg({\|\mathcal{P}^{h}y_{\circ}-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\,{\|r^{h\ell}(\cdot\,;u)\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;(V^{h})^{\prime})}^{2}\bigg).

3.5
The fully discrete approximation

In real computations both the solutions
y^{h}
to (
3.4.4
) and
y^{h\ell}
to (
3.4.23
) can not be computed numerically for any
t\in[0,T]
. This is why we introduce a discretization of the temporal variable to get a fully discrete scheme for the evolution problem (
3.1.4
).

3.5.1
Temporal and Galerkin discretization

Let
0=t_{1}<t_{2}<\ldots<t_{n}=T
be a given grid in
[0,T]
with step sizes
\delta t_{j}=t_{j}-t_{j-1}
for
2\leq j\leq n
. We set

\displaystyle\delta t=\min_{2\leq j\leq n}\delta t_{j}\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta t=\max_{2\leq j\leq n}\delta t_{j}.

We make use of the next hypotheses.

Assumption 3.5.1
.

1)

Assumptions
3.3.1
and
3.4.1
hold.

3)

There exists a constant
\zeta>0
which is independent of
n
, such that

\frac{\Delta t}{\delta t}\leq\zeta,

(3.5.1)

i.e., we have
\Delta t=\mathcal{O}(\delta t)
.

Let us introduce the finite difference quotient

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}y^{h}_{j}=\frac{y_{j}^{h}-y_{j-1}^{h}}{\delta t_{j}}\quad\text{for }j=2,\ldots,n

for the temporal derivative, where
\{y_{j}^{h}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset V^{h}
are approximations for the solution
y^{h}
to (
3.4.4
) at the time instances
t=t_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,n
. Moreover, we define for any
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
the dual elements
\{g_{j}(u)\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset V^{\prime}
by

\displaystyle g_{j}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\delta t_{1}}\int_{0}^{\delta t_{1}/2}(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,

\displaystyle g_{j}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\delta t_{j-1}+\delta t_{j}}\int_{t_{j}-\delta t_{j-1}/2}^{t_{j}+\delta t_{j}/2}(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\quad\text{for }j=2,\ldots,n-1,

\displaystyle g_{n}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\delta t_{n}}\int_{T-\delta t_{n}/2}(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.

To solve (
3.4.4
) by time discretization, we apply the so-called
\theta
-scheme
with
\theta\in[0,1]
, for which the sequence
\{y^{h}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset V^{h}
has to satisfy

{\langle\overline{\partial}y^{h}_{j},\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{H}+\theta a(t_{j};y^{h}_{j},\varphi^{h})+(1-\theta)a(t_{j-1};y^{h}_{j-1},\varphi^{h})={\langle\theta g_{j}+(1-\theta)g_{j-1},\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

(3.5.2a)

for all
\varphi^{h}\in V^{h}
, for
2\leq j\leq n
and

y^{h}_{1}=\mathcal{P}^{h}y_{\circ},

(3.5.2b)

where
\mathcal{P}^{h}:H\to V^{h}
is an appropriate linear projection operator (see Example
3.4.4
). If we choose
\theta=1
, the
\theta
-method yields the
implicit Euler method
, whereas the
explicit Euler method
is obtained if we set
\theta=0
. Taking
\theta=1/2
leads to the
Crank-Nicolson method
.

From
y_{j}^{h}\in V^{h}
,
j=1,\ldots,n
, we infer that

y^{h}_{j}=\sum_{l=1}^{m}\mathrm{y}_{jl}^{h}\varphi_{l}^{h}\in V^{h}\quad\text{for }1\leq j\leq n

(3.5.3)

with coefficient vectors
\mathrm{y}^{h}_{j}=(\mathrm{y}_{jl}^{h})_{1\leq l\leq m}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Inserting (
3.5.3
) into (
3.5.2
) and choosing
\varphi^{h}=\varphi_{i}^{h}
,
i=1,\ldots,m
, we find that the sequence
\{\mathrm{y}_{j}^{h}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
of coefficient vectors satisfies the following initial value problem

\displaystyle\big({\bm{M}}^{h}+\theta\delta t_{j}{\bm{A}}^{h}_{j}\big)\mathrm{y}_{j}^{h}

\displaystyle=\big({\bm{M}}^{h}+(\theta-1)\delta t_{j}{\bm{A}}^{h}_{j-1}\big)\mathrm{y}^{h}_{j-1}+\delta t_{j}\big(\theta\mathrm{g}_{j}^{h}(u)+(1-\theta)\mathrm{g}_{j-1}^{h}(u)\big)

(3.5.4a)

\displaystyle{\bm{M}}^{h}\mathrm{y}^{h}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{y}_{\circ}^{h}

(3.5.4b)

for
2\leq j\leq n
, where
{\bm{M}}^{h}
has been introduced in Example
3.4.4
,
{\bm{A}}^{h}_{j}={\bm{A}}^{h}(t_{j})
(cf. (
3.4.14
)) and
\mathrm{g}_{j}^{h}(u)=({\langle g_{j}(u),\varphi_{i}^{h}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V})_{1\leq i\leq m}
for
1\leq i\leq m
. To ease the presentation we consider only the case
\theta=1
, i.e., the implicit Euler method. We refer the reader for the Crank-Nicolson method to
[
KV02a
]
. The next result is proved in Section
3.8.4
.

Theorem 3.5.2
.

Suppose that Assumption
3.5.1
and
\theta=1
hold. Then there exists a unique sequence
\{y^{h}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset V^{h}
to (
3.5.2
). If
\delta t
is sufficiently small, we have the a-priori bounds

{\|y_{j}^{h}\|}_{H}^{2}\leq e^{-C_{1}(j-1)\delta t}\bigg({\|\mathcal{P}^{h}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\sum_{l=2}^{j}\delta t_{l}\,{\|g_{l}(u)\|}_{V^{\prime}}^{2}\bigg)

(3.5.5)

for
j=2,\ldots,n
with the constant
C_{1}=2\gamma_{1}/c_{V}^{2}
and

{\|y_{n}^{h}\|}_{H}^{2}+\sum_{j=2}^{n}{\|y_{j}^{h}-y_{j-1}^{h}\|}_{H}^{2}+\gamma_{1}\sum_{j=2}^{n}\delta t_{j}\,{\|y_{j}^{h}\|}_{V}^{2}\leq{\|\mathcal{P}^{h}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\sum_{j=2}^{n}\delta t_{j}\,{\|g_{j}(u)\|}_{V^{\prime}}^{2}.

(3.5.6)

3.5.2
A-priori error analysis

In the following theorem we estimate the error between the exact solution and its approximation computed by the implicit Euler method together with a Galerkin discretization. The proof is given in Section
3.8.4
.

Theorem 3.5.3
.

Suppose that Assumption
3.5.1
is satisfied and that
\theta=1
is chosen. Assume that
y
and
y^{h}
are the solutions to (
3.2.4
) and (
3.5.2
), respectively, with
y\in H^{2}(0,T;\mathscr{W})
. Let
\mathcal{P}^{h}
satisfy (
3.4.3
) and
\|\mathcal{P}^{h}\|_{\mathscr{L}(X,H)}\leq C
for a constant
C
independent of
h
. Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that the a-priori error estimate

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta t_{j}\,{\|y(t_{j})-y^{h}_{j}\|}_{V}^{2}\leq C\,\big(\Delta t^{2}+h^{2}+\Delta t^{2}h^{2}\big)

holds.

Remark 3.5.4
.

From Example
3.4.4
it follows that

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{P}^{h}\|}_{\mathscr{L}(X,H)}\leq c_{V}\quad\left\{\begin{aligned} &\text{for }X=H\text{ and }\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H},\\
&\text{for }X=V\text{ and }\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}.\end{aligned}\right.

This,
\|\mathcal{P}^{h}\|_{\mathscr{L}(X,H)}
be bounded independent of
h
.
\blacksquare

3.5.3
POD basis computation

Let Assumption
3.5.1
hold. By

y^{h}_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{y}^{h}_{ji}\varphi_{i}^{h}\quad\text{for }1\leq j\leq n

(3.5.7)

we denote the Galerkin solution to (
3.5.2
) for
\theta=1
. We introduce the coefficient vectors
\mathrm{y}^{h}_{j}=(\mathrm{y}^{h}_{ji})_{1\leq i\leq m}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
for
j=1,\ldots,n
. Let us choose
K=2
,
\omega_{1}^{K}=\omega_{2}^{K}=1
and

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}^{n}=\frac{\delta t_{1}}{2},\quad\alpha_{j}^{n}=\frac{\delta t_{j}+\delta t_{j+1}}{2}\text{ for }j=2,\ldots,n-1,\quad\alpha_{n}^{n}=\frac{\delta t_{n}}{2}.

It follows from Assumption
3.5.1
-3) and
\Delta t\geq\alpha_{j}^{n}
that

\delta t_{j}\geq\delta t=\frac{\delta t\Delta t}{\Delta t}\geq\frac{\Delta t}{\zeta}\geq\frac{\alpha_{j}^{n}}{\zeta}\quad\text{for }j=1,\ldots,n.

(3.5.8)

We further choose the
snapshots

\displaystyle y_{j}^{1}=y_{j}^{h}\in V^{h}\subset X\text{ for }1\leq j\leq n\quad\text{and}\quad y_{1}^{2}=0,~y_{j}^{2}=\overline{\partial}y_{j}^{h}\in V^{h}\subset X\text{ for }j=2,\ldots,n

with the difference quotient
\overline{\partial}y_{j}^{h}=(y_{j}^{h}-y_{j-1}^{h})/\delta t_{j}
. Then
