\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\Big\|y_{j}^{h}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{h},\psi_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}^{n}\Big\|_{X}^{2}+\sum_{j=2}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\Big\|\overline{\partial}y_{j}^{h}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle\overline{\partial}y_{j}^{h},\psi_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}^{n}\Big\|_{X}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\Big\|y_{j}^{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}^{n}\Big\|_{X}^{2}.

We define the
snapshot space

\displaystyle\mathscr{V}^{n}=\Span\big\{y_{j}^{k}\in V\,\big|\,1\leq j\leq n\text{ and }1\leq k\leq 2\big\}

and set
d^{n}=\dim\mathscr{V}^{n}\leq 2n
. Now we compute a POD basis of rank
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d^{n}\}
by solving

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\min\sum_{k=1}^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\Big\|y_{j}^{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}^{n}\Big\|_{X}^{2}\\
&\hskip 2.84526pt\text{s.t. }\{\psi_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset V^{h}\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i}^{n},\psi_{j}^{n}\rangle}_{X}=\delta_{ij},~1\leq i,j\leq\ell\end{aligned}\right.

(3.5.9)

for
X=H
and
X=V
. Utilizing the symmetric and positive weighting matrix
{\bm{W}}=((\langle\varphi_{j}^{h},\varphi_{i}^{h}\rangle_{X}))\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
and the associated weighted inner product we can express the minimization problem as

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\min\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\Big|\mathrm{y}_{j}^{h}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle\mathrm{y}_{j}^{h},\uppsi_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}\,\uppsi_{i}^{n}\Big|_{\bm{W}}^{2}+\sum_{j=2}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\Big|\overline{\partial}\mathrm{y}_{j}^{h}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle\overline{\partial}\mathrm{y}_{j}^{h},\uppsi_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}\,\uppsi_{i}^{n}\Big|_{\bm{W}}^{2}\\
&\hskip 2.84526pt\text{s.t. }\{\uppsi_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\text{ and }{\langle\uppsi_{i}^{n},\uppsi_{j}^{n}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}=\delta_{ij},\quad 1\leq i,j\leq\ell,\end{aligned}\right.

(3.5.10)

where now
\overline{\partial}\mathrm{y}_{j}^{h}=(\mathrm{y}_{j}^{h}-\mathrm{y}_{j-1}^{h})/\delta t_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Recall a result from Section
2.4.2
: Let the eigenvalue-eigenfunction pairs
\{(\lambda_{i}^{nH},\psi_{i}^{nH})\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
and
\{(\lambda_{i}^{nV},\psi_{i}^{nV})\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
satisfy

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{H}^{n}\psi_{i}^{nH}=\lambda_{i}^{nH}\psi_{i}^{nH}\text{ for }i\in\mathbb{I},

\displaystyle\quad\lambda_{1}^{nH}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{d^{n}}^{H}>\lambda_{d^{n}+1}^{nH}=\ldots=0,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{V}^{n}\psi_{i}^{nV}=\lambda_{i}^{nV}\psi_{i}^{nV}\text{ for }i\in\mathbb{I},

\displaystyle\quad\lambda_{1}^{nV}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{d^{n}}^{nV}>\lambda_{d^{n}+1}^{nV}=\ldots=0,

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{H}^{n}\psi

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\langle y^{h}_{j},\psi\rangle}_{H}y_{j}^{h}+\sum_{j=2}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\langle\overline{\partial}y^{h}_{j},\psi\rangle}_{H}\overline{\partial}y_{j}^{h}

\displaystyle\text{for }\psi\in H,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{V}^{n}\psi

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\langle y^{h}_{j},\psi\rangle}_{V}y_{j}^{h}+\sum_{j=2}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\langle\overline{\partial}y^{h}_{j},\psi\rangle}_{V}\overline{\partial}y_{j}^{h}

\displaystyle\text{for }\psi\in V.

Then
\{\psi_{i}^{nH})\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
solves (
3.5.9
) for
X=H
and
\{\psi_{i}^{nV})\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
is the solution to (
3.5.9
) for
X=V
. Moreover, we find the POD approximation error as follows

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\Big\|y_{j}^{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{i}^{nH}\rangle}_{H}\,\psi_{i}^{nH}\Big\|_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\lambda_{i}^{nH},

(3.5.11)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\Big\|y_{j}^{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{k},\psi_{i}^{nV}\rangle}_{V}\,\psi_{i}^{nV}\Big\|_{V}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\lambda_{i}^{nV}.

Remark 3.5.5
.

Let us motivate why we include the finite difference quotients
\{\overline{\partial}y_{j}^{h}\}_{j=2}^{n}
into the set of snapshots; cf.
[
KV02b
, Remark 1]
. While the finite difference quotients are contained in the span of
\{y_{j}^{h}\}_{j=1}^{n}
, the POD bases differ depending on whether
\{\overline{\partial}y_{j}^{h}\}_{j=2}^{n}
are included or not. The linear dependence does not constitute a difficulty for the singular value decomposition which is required to compute the POD basis. In fact, the snapshots themselves can be linearly dependent. Secondly, in anticipation of the rate of convergence results that will be obtained further below, we note that the time derivative of
y
in (
3.4.4a
) must be approximated by the Galerkin-POD based scheme. In case the terms
\{\overline{\partial}y_{j}^{h}\}_{j=2}^{n}
are included in the snapshot ensemble, we will be able to utilize the estimate

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\Big\|y_{j}^{h}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{h},\psi_{i}^{h}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}^{h}\Big\|_{X}^{2}+\sum_{j=2}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\Big\|\overline{\partial}y_{j}^{h}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle\overline{\partial}y_{j}^{h},\psi_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}^{n}\Big\|_{X}^{2}=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\lambda_{i}^{n}.

(3.5.12)

Otherwise, if only the snapshots
\{y_{j}^{h}\}_{j=1}^{n}
are used, we only have

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\alpha_{j}^{n}}{\delta t_{j}^{2}}\,\Big\|y_{j}^{h}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{h},\psi_{i}^{h}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}^{h}\Big\|_{X}^{2}=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\lambda_{i}^{n}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=2}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\Big\|\overline{\partial}y_{j}^{h}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle\overline{\partial}y_{j}^{h},\psi_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}^{n}\Big\|_{X}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\alpha_{j}^{n}}{\delta t_{j}^{2}}\,\bigg(\Big\|y_{j}^{h}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{h},\psi_{i}^{h}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}^{h}\Big\|_{X}^{2}-\Big\|y_{j-1}^{h}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j-1}^{h},\psi_{i}^{h}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}^{h}\Big\|_{X}^{2}\bigg)\leq\frac{2}{\delta t^{2}}\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\lambda_{i}^{n}

which in contrast to (
3.5.12
) contains the factor
\delta t^{-2}
on the right-hand side.
\blacksquare

To compute the POD basis numerically, we follow Section
2.1.10
and set

\displaystyle{\bm{Y}}^{1}

\displaystyle=\big[\mathrm{y}_{1}^{h}\,|\ldots|\,\mathrm{y}_{n}^{h}\big]\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n},

\displaystyle{\bm{Y}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\big[0\,|\,\overline{\partial}\mathrm{y}_{2}^{h}\,|\ldots|\,\overline{\partial}\mathrm{y}_{n}^{h}\big]\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n},

\displaystyle{\bm{D}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{diag}\,(\alpha_{1}^{n},\ldots,\alpha_{n}^{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},

\displaystyle\tilde{{\bm{D}}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{diag}\,({\bm{D}},{\bm{D}})\in\mathbb{R}^{(2n)\times(2n)},

\displaystyle{\bm{Y}}

\displaystyle=\big[{\bm{Y}}^{1}\,|\,{\bm{Y}}^{2}\big]\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times(2n)},

\displaystyle\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}

\displaystyle={\bm{W}}^{1/2}{\bm{Y}}\tilde{{\bm{D}}}^{1/2}.

Then a solution to (
3.5.10
) is given by
\uppsi_{i}^{n}={\bm{W}}^{-1/2}\tilde{\uppsi}_{i}^{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
i=1,\ldots,\ell
, where the
\tilde{\uppsi}_{i}^{n}
’s is obtained computing the singular value decomposition of
\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}
; see Algorithm
2.1.10
.

If
\{\uppsi_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
solves (
3.5.10
) then
\{\psi_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
is a solution to (
3.5.9
) with

\psi_{i}^{n}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\big(\uppsi_{i}^{n}\big)_{j}\varphi_{i}^{h}\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,\ell.

(3.5.13)

On the other hand, if
\{\psi_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
is a solution to (
3.5.9
) then we infer from
\psi_{i}^{n}\in V^{h}
the existence of a uniquely determined matrix
{\bm{\Psi}}=[\uppsi_{1}^{n}\,|\ldots|\,\uppsi_{\ell}^{n}]\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times\ell}
satisfying (
3.5.13
). It follows that the columns
\{\uppsi_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of
{\bm{\Psi}}
solve (
3.5.10
).

3.5.4
POD Galerkin scheme

Suppose that we have computed a POD basis
\{\psi_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d^{n}\}
. We set

\displaystyle X^{n\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\psi_{1}^{n},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}^{n}\big\}\subset X\subset V

and

\displaystyle H^{n\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\psi_{1}^{nH},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}^{nH}\big\},\quad V^{n\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\psi_{1}^{nV},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}^{nV}\big\}

for the choices
X=H
and
X=V
, respectively. To simplify the presentation we utilize the same time grid
\{t_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
in (
3.5.2
), (
3.5.10
) and the POD Galerkin scheme introduced below next. We replace (
3.5.4
) (with
\theta=1
) by the following POD Galerkin scheme: Find
\{y^{h\ell}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset X^{n\ell}
satisfying

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\langle\overline{\partial}y^{h\ell}_{j},\psi\rangle}_{H}+a(t_{j};y^{h\ell}_{j},\psi)={\langle g^{h}_{j}(u),\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\text{ for all }\psi\in X^{n\ell}

(3.5.14a)

for
2\leq j\leq n
and

y^{h\ell}_{1}=\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}y_{\circ}.

(3.5.14b)

Recall the four different choices for
\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}
introduced in (
2.4.11
).

From
\{y_{j}^{h\ell}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset X^{n\ell}
we infer that there are uniquely determined coefficient vectors
\mathrm{y}_{j}^{h\ell}=(\mathrm{y}_{ji}^{h\ell})_{1\leq i\leq m}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
,
1\leq j\leq n
, satisfying

y_{j}^{h\ell}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{y}_{ji}^{h\ell}\psi_{i}^{n}\quad\text{for }j=1,\ldots,n.

(3.5.15)

Inserting (
3.5.15
) into (
3.5.14
), choosing
\psi=\psi_{i}^{n}
,
1\leq i\leq\ell
, and using the notation introduced in Section
3.4.4
we obtain

\displaystyle\big({\bm{M}}^{h\ell}+\delta t_{j}{\bm{A}}^{h\ell}_{j}\big)\mathrm{y}_{j}^{h\ell}

\displaystyle={\bm{M}}^{h\ell}\mathrm{y}^{h\ell}_{j-1}+\delta t_{j}\mathrm{g}_{j}^{h\ell}(u)\text{ for }2\leq j\leq n,

(3.5.16a)

\displaystyle{\bm{M}}^{h\ell}\mathrm{y}^{h}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{y}_{\circ}^{h\ell},

(3.5.16b)

where
{\bm{A}}^{h\ell}_{j}={\bm{A}}^{h\ell}(t_{j})
and
\mathrm{g}^{h\ell}_{j}(u)=\mathrm{g}_{j}^{h}(u)\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
for
1\leq j\leq n
.

In the following theorem, an existence result and a-priori estimates for the solution
\{y^{h\ell}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
are established. Since
{\bm{M}}^{h\ell}+\delta t_{j}{\bm{A}}^{h\ell}_{j}
is regular, the proof follows by the same arguments as in the proof of Theorem
3.5.2
.

Theorem 3.5.6
.

Suppose that Assumption
3.5.1
and
\theta=1
hold. The POD basis
\{\psi_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d^{n}\}
is computed as described in Section
3.5.3
. Then there exists a unique sequence
\{y^{h\ell}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset V^{h}
to (
3.5.14
) provided
\Delta t
is sufficiently small. If
\delta t
is sufficiently small, the following estimates are satisfied:

{\|y_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}\leq e^{-C_{1}(j-1)\delta t}\bigg({\|\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\sum_{l=2}^{j}\delta t_{l}\,{\|g_{l}^{h}(u)\|}_{V^{\prime}}^{2}\bigg)

(3.5.17)

for
j=2,\ldots,n
with the constant
C_{1}=2\gamma_{1}/c_{V}^{2}
and

{\|y_{n}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+\sum_{j=2}^{n}{\|y_{j}^{h\ell}-y_{j-1}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+\gamma_{1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta t_{j}\,{\|y_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}\leq{\|\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\sum_{j=2}^{n}\delta t_{j}\,{\|g_{j}^{\ell}(u)\|}_{V^{\prime}}^{2}.

(3.5.18)

Remark 3.5.7
.

We can avoid the assumption that
\Delta t
has to be sufficiently small. If
\gamma_{2}=0
holds in (
3.2.1b
) (cf. Assumption
3.2.1
-2-3)), this follows arguing as in the proof of Theorem
3.5.6
. If
\gamma_{2}>0
holds true, we can transform our evolution problem (
3.2.4
) as described in Remark
4.1.3
.
\blacksquare

3.5.5
POD a-priori error analysis

Utilizing Theorem
3.5.3
we find that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|y(t_{j})-y^{h\ell}_{j}\|}_{V}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|y(t_{j})-y^{h}_{j}\|}_{V}^{2}+2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|y^{h}_{j}-y^{h\ell}_{j}\|}_{V}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\big(h^{2}+\Delta t^{2}+\Delta t^{2}h^{2}\big)+2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|y^{h}_{j}-y^{h\ell}_{j}\|}_{V}^{2}.

Thus, our goal is to derive an a-priori error estimate for the term

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|y^{h}_{j}-y^{h\ell}_{j}\|}_{V}^{2}.

For the a-priori error analysis we proceed as in Section
3.3.2
and utilize the rate of convergence results presented in Theorem
2.4.17
. Thus, we make use of the following decomposition (compare (
3.3.12
))

\displaystyle y^{h}_{j}-y^{h\ell}_{j}

\displaystyle=y^{h}_{j}-\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}y^{h}_{j}+\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}y^{h}_{j}-y^{h\ell}_{j}=\varrho^{h\ell}_{j}+\vartheta^{h\ell}_{j}\quad\text{for }1\leq j\leq n

with
\varrho^{h\ell}_{j}=y^{h}_{j}-\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}y^{h}_{j}\in(X^{n\ell})^{\bot}
and
\vartheta^{h\ell}_{j}=\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}y^{h}_{j}-y^{h\ell}_{j}\in X^{n\ell}
. Applying the notation introduced in Section
3.5.4
we find

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\left({\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}+{\|\overline{\partial}\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}\right)=\sum_{k=1}^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|y_{j}^{k}-\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}y_{j}^{k}\|}_{V}^{2}.

(3.5.19)

To achieve that

\vartheta^{h\ell}_{1}=\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}y^{h}_{1}-y^{h\ell}_{1}=\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}\big(\mathcal{P}^{h}y_{\circ}\big)-\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}y_{\circ}=0

(3.5.20)

we have to choose appropriate
\mathcal{P}^{h}
and
\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}
in (
3.4.4b
) and (
3.4.23b
), respectively. Recall the choices made in Remark
3.4.11
in order to ensure (
3.5.20
):

1)

X=H
,
\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}_{H}
and
\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}
,

2)

X=H
,
\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}_{V}
and
\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}
,

3)

X=V
,
\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{n\ell}_{H}
and
\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}
,

4)

X=V
,
\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{n\ell}_{V}
and
\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}
.

The next lemma is shown in Section
3.8.4
.

Lemma 3.5.8
.

Let Assumption
3.5.1
and
\theta=1
hold. Suppose that
\{y^{h}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset V^{h}
solves (
3.5.2
). The POD basis
\{\psi_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d^{n}\}
is computed as described in Section
3.5.3
and
\{y^{h\ell}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset V^{h}
is the solution to (
3.5.14
). Then
\vartheta^{h\ell}_{j}=\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}y^{h}_{j}-y^{h\ell}_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq n
satisfies

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}\leq C\sum_{k=1}^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|y_{j}^{k}-\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}y_{j}^{k}\|}_{V}^{2}

(3.5.21)

with a constant depending on
\gamma
,
\gamma_{1}
,
c_{V}
,
T
and
\zeta
.

Now, the following a-priori error follows directly from Proposition
2.4.17
, (
3.5.19
), Lemma
3.5.8
and from

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|y^{h}_{j}-y^{h\ell}_{j}\|}_{V}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|\vartheta^{h\ell}_{j}\|}_{V}^{2}+2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|\varrho^{h\ell}_{j}\|}_{V}^{2}\leq C_{\circ}\sum_{k=1}^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|y_{j}^{k}-\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}y_{j}^{k}\|}_{V}^{2}

with
C_{\circ}=2(C+1)
.

Theorem 3.5.9
.

Let Assumption
3.5.1
and
\theta=1
hold. Suppose that
\{y^{h}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset V^{h}
solves (
3.5.2
). The POD basis
\{\psi_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d^{n}\}
is computed as described in Section
3.5.3
and
\{y^{h\ell}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset V^{h}
is the solution to (
3.5.14
). Then we have the following a-priori error estimates:

1)

For
X=H
,
\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}_{H}
and
\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|y^{h}_{j}-y^{h\ell}_{j}\|}_{V}^{2}\leq C\sum\limits_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\lambda_{i}^{nH}\,{\|\psi_{i}^{nH}\|}_{V}^{2}.

2)

For
X=H
,
\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}_{V}
and
\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}
it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|y^{h}_{j}-y^{h\ell}_{j}\|}_{V}^{2}\leq C\sum\limits_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\lambda_{i}^{nH}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{nH}-\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}_{V}\psi_{i}^{nH}\big\|_{V}^{2}.

3)

For
X=V
,
\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{n\ell}_{H}
and
\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}
we derive

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|y^{h}_{j}-y^{h\ell}_{j}\|}_{V}^{2}\leq C\sum\limits_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\lambda_{i}^{nV}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{nV}-\mathcal{Q}^{n\ell}\psi_{i}^{nV}\big\|_{V}^{2}.

4)

For
X=V
,
\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{n\ell}_{V}
and
\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}
and we get

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|y^{h}_{j}-y^{h\ell}_{j}\|}_{V}^{2}\leq C\sum\limits_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\lambda_{i}^{nV}.

Example 3.5.10
.

We want to verify the a-priori estimates in Theorem
3.5.9
for the guiding model solved with a fix control
u
, which is generated randomly. To make more relevant the use of different projections, we consider
\kappa=0.02
, a time-dependent velocity field
{\bm{v}}(t,{\bm{x}})=((0.1+0.1t)(x_{2}-0.5),(0.1+0.1t)(0.5-x_{1}))
and an initial guess
y_{\circ}({\bm{x}})=x_{1}+x_{2}
. The other parameters are set as in Section
1.3
.

Figure 3.5.1
:
Example
3.5.10
. Plot of the a-priori estimate and the approximation error of the reduced order model for different choices of spaces and projections (y-axis: logarithmic scale).

As one can see from Figure
3.5.1
, the a-priori error estimate predicts the behaviour of the error as soon as
\ell
increases. Note that the constant
C
is not always computable and it depends on the choice of the spaces
H
and
V
. Therefore, we report only the quantities on the right-hand side of the estimates in Theorem
3.5.9
, without the multiplication of the constant
C
. This is the reason of the underestimation for small
\ell
in case 4). Moreover, this justify the use of an a-priori estimate for predicting the qualitative behaviour of the reduced order model rather than the quantitative one and, thus, to choose the dimension
\ell
of the reduced space.
\blacklozenge

Extensions

To derive the rate of convergence results presented in Theorem
3.5.9
we have utilized the snapshots
\{y^{h}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
and
\{\overline{\partial}y^{h}_{j}\}_{j=2}^{n}
for the POD basis computation; cf. Section
3.5.3
. Next we show that we can omit the difference quotients
\{\overline{\partial}y^{h}_{j}\}_{j=2}^{n}
, but we still can derive a rate of convergence result. For that purpose we consider the
H
-orthonormal projection operators
\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}_{H}
and
\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{n\ell}_{H}
; cf. Theorem
3.3.17
and (
3.4.27
). Moreover we choose
\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}
in (
3.5.2b
). Instead of (
3.5.9
) we compute the POD basis of rank
\ell
by solving

\displaystyle\min\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\Big\|y_{j}^{h}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{h},\psi_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}^{n}\Big\|_{X}^{2}\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\{\psi_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset V^{h}\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i}^{n},\psi_{j}^{n}\rangle}_{X}=\delta_{ij},~1\leq i,j\leq\ell.

In Section
3.8.4
we prove the following result.

Theorem 3.5.11
.

Let Assumption
3.5.1
and
\theta=1
hold. Suppose that
\{y^{h}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset V^{h}
solves (
3.5.2
) with
\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}
. The POD basis
\{\psi_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d^{n}\}
is computed as described in Section
3.5.3
and
\{y^{h\ell}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset V^{h}
is the solution to (
3.5.14
). Then the a-priori error estimate

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}{\|y^{h}(t)-y^{h\ell}(t)\|}_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{\leq}~C\cdot\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\lambda_{i}^{nH}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{nH}\big\|_{V}^{2},&&X=H,~\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}_{H},\\
&\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\lambda_{i}^{nV}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{nV}-\mathcal{Q}^{n\ell}_{H}\psi_{i}^{nV}\big\|_{V}^{2},&&X=V,~\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{n\ell}_{H}\end{aligned}\right.

holds with a constant
C>0
.

3.5.6
POD a-posteriori error analysis

Suppose that
\{y^{h}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset V^{h}
and
\{y^{h\ell}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset X^{h\ell}
are the solutions to (
3.5.2
) and (
3.5.14
), respectively. In Theorems
3.5.9
and
3.5.11
we have presented a-priori error estimates for the difference
y_{h}^{j}-y_{j}^{h\ell}
,
j=1,\ldots,n
. However, the convergence rates are only valid if the POD basis is computed utilizing the solution
\{y^{h}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
and – in case of Theorem
3.5.9
– also the difference quotients
\{\overline{\partial}y^{h}_{j}\}_{j=2}^{n}
as snapshots. If the POD basis is computed from different snapshots, the a-priori error estimates does not hold. In this case we need an a-posteriori error estimate which indicates whether the computed POD solution
\{y^{h\ell}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
is sufficiently accurate or not.

In the next theorem we present an a-posteriori error analysis for the fully discrete Galerkin scheme (
3.5.14
). The proof is based on the arguments used in Section
3.4.6
; cf. Section
3.8.4
.

Theorem 3.5.12
.

Let us assume that Assumption
3.5.1
and
\theta=1
hold. Suppose that
\{y^{h}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset V^{h}
solves (
3.5.2
). The POD basis
\{\psi_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d^{n}\}
is computed as described in Section
3.5.3
and
\{y^{h\ell}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset V^{h}
is the POD Galerkin solution to (
3.5.14
). If
\delta t
is sufficiently small, the a-posteriori error estimates

\displaystyle{\|y_{j}^{h}-y_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{e^{-2\gamma_{1}(j-1)\delta t/c_{V}^{2}}}{\gamma_{1}}\,\Delta t\sum_{l=2}^{j}\,{\|r_{l}^{h\ell}\|}_{(V^{h})^{\prime}}^{2}\quad\text{for }2\leq j\leq n,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|y_{j}^{h}-y_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\zeta}{\gamma_{1}^{2}}\sum_{j=2}^{n}\delta t_{j}\,{\|r_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{(V^{h})^{\prime}}^{2}

hold, where the residual vectors are given for
j=2,\ldots,n
by

r_{j}^{h\ell}={\langle\overline{\partial}y_{j}^{h\ell},\cdot\rangle}_{H}+a(t_{j};y^{h\ell}_{j},\cdot)-{\langle(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(t_{j}),\cdot\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\in(V^{h})^{\prime}.

(3.5.22)

Example 3.5.13
.

Similarly to Example
3.5.10
, we want to verify the a-posteriori error estimate in Theorem
3.5.12
for the guiding example. We set all the parameters of the problem as in Example
3.5.10
. The only difference is that we generate the snapshots with a random control
u_{1}
and compute the solution of the reduced order model as well as the a-posteriori error estimate for a second random control
u_{2}
.

Figure 3.5.2
:
Example
3.5.13
. Plot of the a-posteriori estimate and the approximation error of the reduced order model for different choices of spaces and projections (y-axis: logarithmic scale).

According to Figure
3.5.2
and as expected, there is a bad approximation of the full order solution of the problem for small
\ell
. This is mainly caused by the advection
{\bm{v}}
that generates several different dynamics and the fact that the two random controls
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
may be far from each other. For
\ell=100
in fact the true approximation error is still around
10^{-5}
. Another important issue is the factor of overestimation for the a-posteriori estimate, which is influenced by the quantities
\gamma_{1}
(
=10^{-4}
for this example),
\delta t_{j}
(
=0.025
),
\zeta
(
=1
) and indirectly by the velocity field
{\bm{v}}
through the residual
r_{j}^{h\ell}
; cf. Theorem
3.5.12
. In Figure
3.5.2
one can note that the factor of overestimation is between
10
and
30
for each choice of projections
\mathcal{P}^{h}
and
\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}
and space
X
, thus still acceptable. To have a good a-posteriori error estimate is in fact crucial for a POD-based model order reduction, since it indicates whether the true approximation is expected to be good or not. For further details on this topic we refer to Chapter
4
and Chapter
5
. Note at last that there is no possibility of underestimation, as it is happening instead in Example
3.5.10
. This is clearly because in this example all the quantities for the a-posteriori error estimation are computable. This is another crucial point for judging the good or bad quality of an a-posteriori estimate.
\blacklozenge

3.6
Extension to non-linear evolution problems

The application of the POD method can be extended to non-linear evolution problems. Here we focus on a non-linear problem which arises in fluid dynamics and closely follow Section III-3 in
[
Tem97
]
.

3.6.1
The abstract non-linear evolution problem

Let
V
and
H
be real separable Hilbert spaces and suppose that
V
is dense in
H
with compact embedding. As in Remark
3.2.2
-1, we consider a time-independent bilinear form
a:V\times V\to\mathbb{R}
and associate with it the linear operator
\mathcal{A}:V\to V^{\prime}
by

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{A}\varphi,\phi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}=a(\varphi,\phi)\quad\text{for }\varphi,\phi\in V.

To be able to consider
\mathcal{A}
as an operator mapping from
H
to
H
, its domain is defined as in (
3.2.3
). In contrast to the linear case, we additionally introduce a linear and continuous operator
\mathcal{C}:V\to V^{\prime}
which maps
D(\mathcal{A})
into
H
. Moreover, let
\mathcal{N}:V\to V^{\prime}
be a continuous non-linear function,
y_{\circ}\in V
an initial condition,
\mathcal{F}\in L^{2}(0,T;H)
an inhomogeneity and
u
a control element of the Hilbert space
{\mathscr{U}}
which is mapped by a linear and bounded
control operator

\mathcal{B}:{\mathscr{U}}\to L^{2}(0,T;H)
to a function
\mathcal{B}u\in L^{2}(0,T;H)
. Then we consider the non-linear evolution problem

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle y(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+a(y(t),\varphi)+{\langle\mathcal{C}y(t)+\mathcal{N}(y(t)),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

\displaystyle={\langle(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

(3.6.1)

\displaystyle\text{for all }\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

\displaystyle{\langle y(0),\varphi\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle={\langle y_{\circ},\varphi\rangle}_{H}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in V.

Results about the existence of solutions to (
3.6.1
) depend on the concrete qualities of
a
,
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{N}
. The following hypotheses are taken from
[
Tem97
]
.

Assumption 3.6.1
.

1)

Let
y_{\circ}\in V
,
\mathcal{F}\in L^{2}(0,T;H)
and
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
with
\mathcal{B}u\in L^{2}(0,T;H)
.

2)

We assume that the bilinear form
a
is bounded, i.e. there is a constant
\gamma\geq 0
such that

\big|a(\varphi,\psi)\big|\leq\gamma\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}{\|\psi\|}_{V}\quad\text{for all }\varphi,\psi\in V.

(3.6.2)

Additionally, we also assume that
\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{C}
is coercive

a(\varphi,\varphi)+{\langle\mathcal{C}\varphi,\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\geq\gamma_{1}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}^{2}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in V

(3.6.3)

with a constant
\gamma_{1}>0
.

3)

Let further
\mathcal{C}
satisfy the properties

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{C}\varphi\|}_{H}

\displaystyle\leq c_{\mathcal{C}}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}^{1-\delta_{1}}{\|\mathcal{A}\varphi\|}_{H}^{\delta_{1}}

\displaystyle\text{for all }\varphi\in D(\mathcal{A}),

(3.6.4)

\displaystyle\big|{\langle\mathcal{C}\varphi,\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\big|

\displaystyle\leq c_{\mathcal{C}}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}^{1+\delta_{2}}{\|\varphi\|}_{H}^{1-\delta_{2}}

\displaystyle\text{for all }\varphi\in V,

for a constant
c_{\mathcal{C}}>0
and for
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\in[0,1)
.

4)

For the non-linearity, suppose there is another bilinear and continuous function
\mathcal{M}:V\times V\to V^{\prime}
satisfying
\mathcal{M}(\varphi,\varphi)=\mathcal{N}(\varphi)
for all
\varphi\in V
. Let it map from
V\times D(\mathcal{A})
respectively
D(\mathcal{A})\times V
into
H
such that there exist constants
c_{\mathcal{M}}>0
and
\delta_{3},\delta_{4},\delta_{5}\in[0,1)
with

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{M}(\varphi,\phi),\phi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

\displaystyle=0,

(3.6.5)

\displaystyle\big|{\langle\mathcal{M}(\varphi,\phi),\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\big|

\displaystyle\leq c_{\mathcal{M}}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{H}^{\delta_{3}}{\|\varphi\|}_{V}^{1-\delta_{3}}{\|\phi\|}_{V}{\|\psi\|}_{V}^{\delta_{3}}{\|\psi\|}_{H}^{1-\delta_{3}},

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{M}(\varphi,\chi)\|}_{H}+{\|\mathcal{M}(\chi,\varphi)\|}_{H}

\displaystyle\leq c_{\mathcal{M}}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}{\|\chi\|}_{V}^{1-\delta_{4}}{\|\mathcal{A}\chi\|}_{H}^{\delta_{4}},

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{M}(\varphi,\chi)\|}_{H}

\displaystyle\leq c_{\mathcal{M}}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{H}^{\delta_{5}}{\|\varphi\|}_{V}^{1-\delta_{5}}{\|\chi\|}_{V}^{1-\delta_{5}}{\|\mathcal{A}\chi\|}_{H}^{\delta_{5}}

for all
\varphi,\phi,\psi\in V
and for all
\chi\in D(\mathcal{A})
.

The following result extends Theorem 2.1 in
[
Tem97
, p. 111]
in the sense that the function
f
was not originally assumed to be time-dependent. However, the proof is analogous in every step for our case.

Theorem 3.6.2
.

Assume that Assumption
3.6.1
holds. Then there exists a unique solution of (
3.6.1
) satisfying

y\in C([0,T];V)\cap L^{2}(0,T;D(\mathcal{A})).

(3.6.6)

Let us present an example for the non-linear evolution system (
3.6.1
) under Assumption
3.6.1
.

Example 3.6.3
.

Let
\Omega
denote a bounded domain in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with boundary
\Gamma
and let
T>0
. The two-dimensional
Navier-Stokes equation
is given by

\displaystyle\rho~\big(y_{t}+(y\cdot\nabla)y\big)-\nu\Delta y+\nabla p

\displaystyle=f

\displaystyle\text{in }Q=(0,T)\times\Omega,

(3.6.7a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}~y

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{in }Q,

(3.6.7b)

where
\rho>0
is the density of the fluid,
\nu>0
is the kinematic viscosity,
f
represents volume forces and

\displaystyle(y\cdot\nabla)y=\Big(y_{1}\frac{\partial y_{1}}{\partial x_{1}}+y_{2}\frac{\partial y_{1}}{\partial x_{2}},y_{1}\frac{\partial y_{2}}{\partial x_{1}}+y_{2}\frac{\partial y_{2}}{\partial x_{2}}\Big)^{\top}.

The unknowns are the velocity field
y=(y_{1},y_{2})
and the pressure
p
. Together with (
3.6.7
), one can consider non-slip boundary conditions

y=y_{d}\quad\text{on }\Sigma=(0,T)\times\Gamma

(3.6.7c)

and the initial condition

y(0,\cdot)=y_{\circ}\quad\text{in }\Omega.

(3.6.7d)

In
[
Tem97
, pp. 104-107, 116-117]
it was shown that (
3.6.7
) can be written in the form (
3.6.1
).
\blacklozenge

3.6.2
The continuous POD method

In this subsection we introduce the continuous version of the POD method for (
3.6.1
).

POD Galerkin scheme

Let
y\in L^{2}(0,T;V)
be a solution to (
3.6.1
). We choose the snapshot set

\displaystyle\mathscr{V}=\bigg\{\int_{0}^{T}\varphi(t)y(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\,\Big|\,\varphi\in L^{2}(0,T)\bigg\}\subset V

with dimension

\displaystyle d=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\dim\mathscr{V}&&\text{if }\dim\mathscr{V}<\infty,\\
&\infty&&\text{otherwise.}\end{aligned}\right.

Note that we do not take the time derivatives as snapshots. This is often done in numerical examples. However, the inclusion of the time derivatives is straightforward; cf. Section
2.4.1
. Again,
X
stands either for the separable Hilbert spaces
H
or
V
. Then the POD basis of rank
\ell
is given by

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\min\int_{0}^{T}\Big\|y(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y(t),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{X}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 1.13809pt\text{s.t. }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset X\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{X}=\delta_{ij}\text{ for }1\leq i,j\leq\ell.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.6.8)

A solution to (
3.6.8
) is given by Theorem
2.2.7
. Proceeding as in Section
2.4.1
we obtain the bases
\{\psi_{i}^{H}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
for
X=H
and
\{\psi_{i}^{V}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
for
X=V
with the associated eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{i}^{H}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
and
\{\lambda_{i}^{V}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
, respectively. We set

\displaystyle H^{\ell}=\mathrm{span}\{\psi_{1}^{H},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}^{H}\}\quad\text{and}\quad V^{\ell}=\mathrm{span}\{\psi_{1}^{V},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}^{V}\}.

If we do not want to distinguish between the choices
X=H
and
X=V
, we just write
X^{\ell}=\mathrm{span}\{\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}\}
. Recall that
H^{\ell}\subset V
follows from Lemma
2.4.6
-1).

The POD Galerkin method for (
3.6.1
) reads as follows: find
y^{\ell}:[0,T]\to X^{\ell}
satisfying

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle y^{\ell}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}+a(y^{\ell}(t),\psi)+{\langle\mathcal{C}y^{\ell}(t)+\mathcal{N}(y^{\ell}(t)),\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

\displaystyle={\langle(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(t),\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

(3.6.9)

\displaystyle\text{for all }\psi\in X^{\ell}\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

\displaystyle\hskip 49.79231pty^{\ell}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y_{\circ},

where
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}:X\to X^{\ell}
denotes either the
H
-orthogonal projection
\mathcal{P}_{H}^{\ell}
(in case of
X=H
) or the
H
-orthogonal projection
\mathcal{Q}_{H}^{\ell}
(in case of
X=V
).

Assumption 3.6.4
.

1)

Assumption
3.6.1
holds.

2)

The Hilbert space
X
denotes either
H
or
V
.

Recall also that we have set
{\mathscr{Y}}=W(0,T)
. The next result is proved in Section
3.8.5
.

Proposition 3.6.5
.

Suppose that Assumption
3.6.4
holds. Then the solution to (
3.6.9
) is unique and satisfies

{\|y^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq C\Big({\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}+{\|f\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}+{\|u\|}_{U}\Big)

(3.6.10)

for a constant
C>0
which is independent of
\ell
.

From
y^{\ell}(t)\in V^{\ell}
for almost all
t\in[0,T]
, it follows that

\displaystyle y^{\ell}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{y}_{i}^{\ell}(t)\psi_{i}\quad\text{f.a.a. }t\in[0,T],

with a coefficient vector
\mathrm{y}^{\ell}(t)=(\mathrm{y}_{i}^{\ell}(t))_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
. In Section
3.3.1
we have introduced the mass matrix
{\bm{M}}^{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell\times\ell}
, the vector
\mathrm{g}^{\ell}(t;u)\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
for the right-hand side and the vector
\mathrm{y}_{\circ}^{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
for the initial condition. Moreover, we define the matrix

\displaystyle{\bm{A}}^{\ell}=\big(\big(a(\psi_{j},\psi_{i})+{\langle\mathcal{C}\psi_{j},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\big)\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell\times\ell}.

For the non-linearity we introduce the vector

\displaystyle\mathrm{n}^{\ell}(\mathrm{v})=\bigg(\Big\langle\mathcal{N}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{v}_{j}\psi_{j}\Big),\psi_{i}\Big\rangle_{V^{\prime},V}\bigg)_{1\leq i\leq\ell}

for the vector
\mathrm{v}=(\mathrm{v}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq\ell}
. Then (
3.6.9
) is equivalent to the following non-linear,
\ell
-dimensional system of differential equations

\displaystyle{\bm{M}}^{\ell}\dot{\mathrm{y}}^{\ell}(t)+{\bm{A}}^{\ell}\mathrm{y}^{\ell}(t)+\mathrm{n}^{\ell}\big(\mathrm{y}^{\ell}(t)\big)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{g}^{\ell}(t;u)\quad\text{for }t\in(0,T],

(3.6.11)

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}^{\ell}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{y}_{\circ}^{\ell}.

System (
3.6.11
) is a low-dimensional model for (
3.6.1
).

POD a-priori error analysis

In the next theorem we present an a-priori error estimate for the term

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}{\|y(t)-y^{\ell}(t)\|}_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t,

where
y
and
y^{\ell}
solve (
3.6.1
) and (
3.6.9
), respectively. The proof is based on the results of Proposition
2.4.10
, Section
3.3.2
, (
3.6.2
), (
3.6.4
), (
3.6.3
) and (
3.6.5
). It is given in Section
3.8.5
.

Theorem 3.6.6
.

Suppose that Assumption
3.6.4
holds. Let
y
and
y^{\ell}
be the solutions to (
3.6.1
) and (
3.6.9
), respectively, where we suppose that
y\in H^{1}(0,T;V)
. Then the following a-priori error estimate is valid:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}{\|y(t)-y^{\ell}(t)\|}_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\cdot\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{H}\,{\|\psi_{i}^{H}\|}_{V}^{2},&&X=H,~\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H},\\
&\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{V}\,{\|\psi_{i}^{V}-\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}\psi_{i}^{V}\|}_{H}^{2},&&X=V,~\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}\end{aligned}\right.

for a constant
C>0
which depends on
y
, but not on
\ell
.

3.6.3
The semidiscrete approximation

In this section we extend the approach presented in Section
3.4
to the non-linear evolution problem (
3.6.1
). Our main focus lies on the derivation of the corresponding discretized systems and the application of the POD method. Therefore, we do not proof existence, uniqueness or regularity results. However, we show how the error analysis of Section
3.4
can be transferred to the non-linear case.

Galerkin discretization

In (
3.4.1
) we have introduced the
m
-dimensional subspace
V^{h}\subset V
. We apply a standard Galerkin scheme for (
3.6.1
). Thus, we look for a function
y^{h}
satisfying
y^{h}(t)\in V^{h}
in
[0,T]
almost everywhere and

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle y^{h}(t),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{H}+a(y^{h}(t),\varphi^{h})+{\langle\mathcal{C}y^{h}(t)+\mathcal{N}(y^{h}(t)),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

\displaystyle={\langle(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(t),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

(3.6.12)

\displaystyle\forall\varphi^{h}\in V^{h}\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

\displaystyle y^{h}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}y_{\circ},

where the linear projection operator
\mathcal{P}^{h}:X\to V^{h}
stands for one of the two projections introduced in Example
3.4.4
. Next we derive a system of ordinary differential equations for the coefficients
\mathrm{y}^{h}(t)=(\mathrm{y}_{i}^{h}(t))_{1\leq i\leq m}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
of the Galerkin ansatz

\displaystyle y^{h}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{y}_{i}^{h}(t)\varphi_{i}^{h}\in V^{h}\quad\text{for }t\in[0,T].

In Section
3.4.1
we have introduced the mass matrix
{\bm{M}}^{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
, the vector
\mathrm{g}^{h}(t;u)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
for the right-hand side and the vector
\mathrm{y}_{\circ}^{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
for the initial condition. Moreover, we define the matrix

\displaystyle{\bm{A}}^{h}=\big(\big(a(\varphi_{j},\varphi_{i})+{\langle\mathcal{C}\varphi_{j},\varphi_{i}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\big)\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}.

For the non-linearity we introduce the vector

\displaystyle\mathrm{n}^{h}(\mathrm{v})=\bigg(\Big\langle\mathcal{N}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathrm{v}_{j}\varphi_{j}\Big),\varphi_{i}\Big\rangle_{V^{\prime},V}\bigg)_{1\leq i\leq m}

for the vector
\mathrm{v}=(\mathrm{v}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq m}
. Then (
3.6.9
) is equivalent to the following non-linear,
m
-dimensional system of differential equations

\displaystyle{\bm{M}}^{h}\dot{\mathrm{y}}^{h}(t)+{\bm{A}}^{h}\mathrm{y}^{h}(t)+\mathrm{n}^{h}\big(\mathrm{y}^{h}(t)\big)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{g}^{h}(t;u)\quad\text{for }t\in(0,T],

(3.6.13)

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}^{h}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{y}_{\circ}^{h}.

Here we do not consider the existence of a unique solution to (
3.6.12
); cf. Section
3.4.1
for the semi-discretized linear evolution problem. Moreover, we do not study the a-priori error between a solution to (
3.6.1
) and (
3.6.12
); cf. Section
3.4.2
for the semi-discretized linear evolution problem.

POD Galerkin scheme

Suppose that there exists a unique soultion
y^{h}
to (
3.6.12
). In the context of Section
2.4.1
we choose
K=1
,
\omega_{1}^{K}=1
and
y^{1}=y^{h}
. Since
y^{h}\in L^{2}(0,T;V)
we can apply the results derived by
[
Sin14
]
; as in Section
2.4.1
. Suppose that
\{(\lambda_{i},\psi_{i})\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
denote the eigenvalue-eigenvector pairs solving (
3.4.18
), where the operator
\mathcal{R}:X\to V^{h}\subset X
is given as

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\psi=\int_{0}^{T}{\langle y^{h}(t),\psi\rangle}_{X}\,y^{h}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\quad\text{for }\psi\in X

and
d=\dim\,\{\mathcal{R}\psi\,\big|\,\psi\in X\}\leq m
. Thus,
\mathcal{R}
is a finite rank operator. It follows from
[
Sin14
, Lemma 3.1-2)]
that
d
is the same for the choices
X=H
and
X=V
. Then
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset V^{h}
is a POD basis of rank
\ell
solving

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\min\int_{0}^{T}\Big\|y^{h}(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y^{h}(t),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{X}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 2.84526pt\text{s.t. }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset X\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{X}=\delta_{ij}\text{ for }1\leq i,j\leq\ell.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.6.14)

For
\{(\lambda_{i}^{h},\psi_{i}^{h})\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
satisfying (
3.4.18
) we obtain

\int_{0}^{T}\Big\|y^{h}(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y^{h}(t),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{X}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d}\lambda_{i}.

Following Section
3.4.1
we infer that there is exactly one coefficient matrix
{\bm{\Psi}}=[\uppsi_{1}\,|\ldots\,\uppsi_{\ell}]\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times\ell}
satisfying (
3.4.21
). We define again the positive definite and symmetric matrix
{\bm{W}}=((\langle\varphi_{j}^{h},\varphi_{i}^{h}\rangle_{X}))\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
. Then (
3.6.14
) is equivalent with the minimization problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\min\int_{0}^{T}\Big|\mathrm{y}^{h}(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle\mathrm{y}^{h}(t),\uppsi_{i}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}\,\uppsi_{i}\Big|_{\bm{W}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 2.84526pt\text{s.t. }\{\uppsi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\text{ and }{\langle\uppsi_{i},\uppsi_{j}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}=\delta_{ij}\text{ for }1\leq i,j\leq\ell.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.6.15)

Suppose that we have computed a POD basis
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell
with
\ell\ll d\leq m
. Again, we set

\displaystyle X^{\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}\big\}

and

\displaystyle H^{\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\psi_{1}^{H},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}^{H}\big\},\quad V^{\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\psi_{1}^{V},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}^{V}\big\}

for the choices
X=H
and
X=V
, respectively. As before, we omit the dependence of
\{\psi_{i}^{H}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
and
\{\psi_{i}^{V}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
on
h
for the reader’s convenience. Then we replace (
3.6.12
) by the following POD Galerkin scheme: find
y^{h\ell}:[0,T]\to X^{\ell}
satisfying

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle y^{h\ell}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}+a(y^{h\ell}(t),\psi)+{\langle\mathcal{C}y^{h\ell}(t)+\mathcal{N}(y^{h\ell}(t)),\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

\displaystyle={\langle(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(t),\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

(3.6.16)

\displaystyle\forall\psi\in X^{h\ell}\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

\displaystyle y^{h\ell}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}y_{\circ},

where we choose either
\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}=\mathcal{P}_{H}^{h\ell}
for
X=H
or
\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}=\mathcal{P}_{HV^{h\ell}}^{h\ell}
for
X=V
.

We proceed as in Section
3.4.4
and derive the following non-linear system of ordinary differential equations:

\displaystyle{\bm{M}}^{h\ell}\dot{\mathrm{y}}^{h\ell}(t)+{\bm{A}}^{h\ell}\mathrm{y}^{h\ell}(t)+\mathrm{n}^{h\ell}\big(\mathrm{y}^{h\ell}(t)\big)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{g}^{h\ell}(t;u)\quad\text{for }t\in(0,T],

(3.6.17)

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}^{h\ell}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{y}_{\circ}^{h\ell},

where the mass matrix
{\bm{M}}^{h\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell\times\ell}
as well as the vectors
\mathrm{y}^{h\ell}
,
\mathrm{g}^{h\ell}(t;u)
,
\mathrm{y}_{\circ}^{h\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
have been introduced in Section
3.4.4
. Moreover, we have set

\displaystyle{\bm{A}}^{h\ell}

\displaystyle=\big(\big(a(\psi_{j},\psi_{i})+{\langle\mathcal{C}\psi_{j},\psi_{i}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\big)\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell\times\ell},

\displaystyle\mathrm{n}^{h\ell}(\mathrm{v})

\displaystyle=\bigg(\Big\langle\mathcal{N}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{v}_{j}\psi_{j}\Big),\varphi_{i}\Big\rangle_{V^{\prime},V}\bigg)_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\quad\text{for the vector }\mathrm{v}=\big(\mathrm{v}_{i}\big)_{1\leq i\leq\ell}.

POD a-priori error analysis

Let us suppose the following hypothesis.

Assumption 3.6.7
.

1)

Assumption
3.6.4
holds.

2)

There exists a unique solution
y^{h}
of (
3.6.12
) satisfying

\displaystyle{\|y^{h}\|}_{L^{\infty}(0,T;V)}\leq C\Big({\|\mathcal{P}^{h}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}+{\|f\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}+{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\Big).

3)

The POD basis
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
is computed by solving (
3.6.14
).

4)

There exists a unique solution
y^{h\ell}\in H^{1}(0,T;V)
to the POD Galerkin scheme (
3.6.16
).

Similarly as in Section
3.4.5
we consider the difference

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}{\|y^{h}(t)-y^{h\ell}(t)\|}_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

and present an a-priori error estimate utilizing the error formulas of Section
2.4.2
and the assuption
y^{\ell}\in H^{1}(0,T;V)
. This is stated in the next theorem which is proved in Section
3.8.5
.

Theorem 3.6.8
.

Let Assumption
3.6.7
be satisfied. Then

1)

For
X=H
,
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}
and
\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}
we have

\displaystyle{\|y^{h}-y^{h\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}\leq C\sum\limits_{i=\ell+1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{H}\,{\|\psi_{i}^{H}\big\|}_{V}^{2}.

2)

For
X=V
and
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{V}
and
\mathcal{P}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{V}
we derive

\displaystyle{\|y^{h}-y^{h\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}\leq C\sum\limits_{i=\ell+1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{V}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{V}-\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{V}\psi_{i}^{V}\big\|_{V}^{2}.

POD a-posteriori error analysis

A-posteriori error analysis can be utilized to estimate the difference
y^{h}-y^{h\ell}
without the knowledge of
y^{h}
and without any assumptions on the choice of the POD basis. For that reason we present also a result for our non-linear evolution problem. For almost all
t\in[0,T]
let us define the time dependent residual
r^{h\ell}(t)\in(V^{h})^{\prime}
by

r^{h\ell}(t)=a(y^{h\ell}(t),\cdot)+{\langle y_{t}^{h\ell}(t)+\mathcal{C}y^{h\ell}(t)+\mathcal{N}(y^{h\ell}(t))-(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(t),\cdot\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}.

(3.6.18)

Then the next result holds. For a proof we refer to Section
3.8.5
.

Proposition 3.6.9
.

Suppose that Assumption
3.6.7
holds. By
y^{h}
and
y^{h\ell}
we denote the solutions to (
3.6.12
) and (
3.6.16
), respectively. We assume that
\|y^{h\ell}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}
is bounded independent of
h
and
\ell
. Then the following a-posteriori error estimates hold for almost all
t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle{\|y^{h}(t)-y^{h\ell}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{C}_{1}^{h\ell}(t)\bigg(\mathsf{R}_{\circ}^{h\ell}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathsf{R}_{1}^{h\ell}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\bigg),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}{\|y^{h}(s)-y^{h\ell}(s)\|}_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\mathsf{R}}_{\circ}^{h\ell}+\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\mathsf{R}}_{1}^{h\ell}(s)+\mathsf{R}_{2}^{h\ell}(s)\bigg(\mathsf{R}_{\circ}^{h\ell}+\int_{0}^{s}\mathsf{R}_{1}^{h\ell}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau\bigg)\,\mathrm{d}s,

where

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{1}^{h\ell}(t)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg(\frac{2c_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}}{\gamma_{1}}\int_{0}^{t}{\|y^{h\ell}(s)\|}_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\bigg),\quad\mathsf{R}_{\circ}^{h\ell}={\|(\mathcal{P}^{h}-\mathcal{P}^{\ell})y_{\circ}\|}_{H}^{2},

(3.6.19a)

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{1}^{h\ell}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\gamma_{1}}\,{\|r^{h\ell}(s)\|}_{(V^{h})^{\prime}}^{2},

(3.6.19b)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{R}}_{\circ}^{h\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathsf{R}_{\circ}^{h\ell}}{\gamma_{1}},\quad\tilde{\mathsf{R}}_{1}^{h\ell}(s)=\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\mathsf{R}_{1}^{h\ell}(s),\quad\mathsf{R}_{2}^{h\ell}(s)=\frac{2c_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}}{\gamma_{1}^{2}}\,\mathsf{C}_{1}^{h\ell}(\tau)\,{\|y^{h\ell}(s)\|}_{V}^{2}

(3.6.19c)

for almost all
s\in[0,T]
.

3.6.4
The fully discrete approximation

To solve (
3.6.12
) and (
3.6.16
) numerically, a temporal discretization is required for the interval
[0,T]
. This is done in this subsection.

Temporal and Galerkin discretization

We apply the same time discretization as in Section
3.5.1
: Let
0=t_{1}<t_{2}<\ldots<t_{n}=T
be a given grid in
[0,T]
with step sizes
\delta t_{j}=t_{j}-t_{j-1}
for
2\leq j\leq n
. We set

\displaystyle\delta t=\min_{2\leq j\leq n}\delta t_{j}\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta t=\max_{2\leq j\leq n}\delta t_{j}.

Recall the definition of the functions
\{g_{j}(u)\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset V^{\prime}
, where

\displaystyle g_{j}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\delta t_{1}}\int_{0}^{\delta t_{1}/2}(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,

\displaystyle g_{j}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\delta t_{j-1}+\delta t_{j}}\int_{t_{j}-\delta t_{j-1}/2}^{t_{j}+\delta t_{j}/2}(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\quad\text{for }j=2,\ldots,n-1,

\displaystyle g_{n}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\delta t_{n}}\int_{T-\delta t_{n}/2}(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

for every
u\in\mathcal{U}
. To solve (
3.6.12
) we apply the implicit Euler method. Then the sequence
\{y^{h}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset V^{h}
satisfies

\displaystyle{\langle\overline{\partial}_{t}y^{h}_{j},\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{H}+a(y^{h}_{j},\varphi^{h})+{\langle\mathcal{C}y^{h}_{j}+\mathcal{N}(y^{h}_{j}),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

\displaystyle={\langle g_{j}(u),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

(3.6.20)

\displaystyle\forall\varphi^{h}\in V^{h}\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

\displaystyle y^{h}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{h}_{H}y_{\circ}.

We interprete
y_{j}^{h}
as approximatios for the solution
y^{h}
to (
3.6.12
) at time
t=t_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq n
. From
y_{j}^{h}\in V^{h}
,
j=1,\ldots,n
, we infer that

y^{h}_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{y}_{ji}^{h}\varphi_{i}^{h}\in V^{h}\quad\text{for }1\leq j\leq n

(3.6.21)

with coefficient vectors
\mathrm{y}^{h}_{j}=(\mathrm{y}_{ji}^{h})_{1\leq i\leq m}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Inserting (
3.6.21
) into (
3.6.20
) and choosing
\varphi^{h}=\varphi_{i}^{h}
,
i=1,\ldots,m
, we find that the sequence
\{\mathrm{y}_{j}^{h}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
of coefficient vectors satisfies the following non-linear initial value problem

\displaystyle\big({\bm{M}}^{h}+\delta t_{j}{\bm{A}}^{h}\big)\mathrm{y}_{j}^{h}+\mathrm{n}^{h}(\mathrm{y}^{h}_{j})

\displaystyle={\bm{M}}^{h}\mathrm{y}^{h}_{j-1}+\delta t_{j}\mathrm{g}_{j}^{h}(u)\text{ for }2\leq j\leq n,

(3.6.22a)

\displaystyle{\bm{M}}^{h}\mathrm{y}^{h}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{y}_{\circ}^{h},

(3.6.22b)

where
\mathrm{g}_{j}^{h}(u)=({\langle g_{j}(u),\varphi_{i}^{h}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V})_{1\leq i\leq m}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
for
1\leq j\leq n
. The following result is proved in Section
3.8.5
.

Proposition 3.6.10
.

Let Assumption
3.6.1
hold. If the time step
\delta t
is sufficiently small, there exists a unique sequence
\{y^{h}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset V^{h}
to (
3.6.20
). Furthermore, we have the a-priori estimates

{\|y_{j}^{h}\|}_{H}^{2}\leq e^{-C_{1}(j-1)\delta t}\bigg({\|\mathcal{P}^{h}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\sum_{l=2}^{j}\delta t_{l}\,{\|g_{l}(u)\|}_{V^{\prime}}^{2}\bigg)

(3.6.23)

for
j=2,\ldots,n
with the constant
C_{1}=2\gamma_{1}/c_{V}^{2}
and

{\|y_{n}^{h}\|}_{H}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\|y_{j}^{h}-y_{j-1}^{h}\|}_{H}^{2}+\gamma_{1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta t_{j}\,{\|y_{j}^{h}\|}_{V}^{2}\leq{\|\mathcal{P}^{h}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\sum_{j=2}^{n}\delta t_{j}\,{\|g_{j}(u)\|}_{V^{\prime}}^{2}.

(3.6.24)

POD basis computation

Let
y_{\circ}\in V
,
f\in L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})
and
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
. By

y^{h}_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{y}^{h}_{ji}\varphi_{i}^{h}\quad\text{for }1\leq j\leq n

(3.6.25)

we denote the Galerkin solution to (
3.6.20
). We introduce the coefficient vectors
\mathrm{y}^{h}_{j}=(\mathrm{y}^{h}_{ji})_{1\leq i\leq m}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
for
j=1,\ldots,n
. In Section
3.5.3
we have introduced the positive weighting parameters
\{\alpha^{n}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
. Let us choose
\wp=1
,
\omega_{1}^{\wp}=1
and the snapshots

\displaystyle y_{j}^{1}=y_{j}^{h}\in V^{h}\quad\text{for }j=1,\ldots,n.

The snapshot subspace is given as
\mathscr{V}^{n}=\Span\,\{y_{j}^{h}\,|\,1\leq j\leq n\}\subset V^{h}
with dimension
d^{n}=\dim\mathscr{V}^{n}\leq\min(n,m)
. For either
X=H
or
X=V
we compute a POD basis of rank
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d^{n}\}
by solving

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\min\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\Big\|y_{j}^{h}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{h},\psi_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}^{n}\Big\|_{X}^{2}\\
&\hskip 2.84526pt\text{s.t. }\{\psi_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset V^{h}\subset X\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i}^{n},\psi_{j}^{n}\rangle}_{X}=\delta_{ij},~1\leq i,j\leq\ell.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.6.26)

Recall that
\{\psi_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{n}\psi_{i}^{n}=\lambda_{i}^{n}\psi_{i}^{n}\text{ for }1\leq i\leq\ell,\quad\lambda_{1}^{n}\geq\ldots\lambda_{\ell}^{n}>0,

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{n}\psi=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{n}\,{\langle y_{j}^{h},\psi\rangle}_{X}\,y^{h}_{j}\quad\text{for }\psi\in X

with either
X=H
or
X=V
. Utilizing the symmetric and positive definite weighting matrix
{\bm{W}}=((\langle\varphi_{j}^{h},\varphi_{i}^{h}\rangle_{X}))\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
and the associated weighted inner product we can express the minimization problem as

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\min\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\Big|\mathrm{y}_{j}^{h}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle\mathrm{y}_{j}^{h},\uppsi_{i}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}\,\uppsi_{i}\Big|_{\bm{W}}^{2}\\
&\hskip 2.84526pt\text{s.t. }\{\uppsi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\text{ and }{\langle\uppsi_{i},\uppsi_{j}\rangle}_{\bm{W}}=\delta_{ij},\quad 1\leq i,j\leq\ell.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.6.27)

We follow Section
2.1.10
and set

\displaystyle{\bm{Y}}

\displaystyle=\big[\mathrm{y}_{1}^{h}\,|\ldots|\,\mathrm{y}_{n}^{h}\big]\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n},

\displaystyle{\bm{D}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{diag}\,(\alpha_{1}^{n},\ldots,\alpha_{n}^{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},

\displaystyle\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}

\displaystyle={\bm{W}}^{1/2}{\bm{Y}}\tilde{{\bm{D}}}^{1/2}.

Then a solution to (
3.6.27
) is given by
\uppsi_{i}={\bm{W}}^{-1/2}\tilde{\uppsi}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
i=1,\ldots,\ell
, where the
\tilde{\uppsi}_{i}
’s solve the symmetric
m\times m
eigenvalue problem

\displaystyle\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}^{\top}\tilde{\uppsi}_{i}=\lambda_{i}^{n}\tilde{\uppsi}_{i}\quad\text{with }\lambda_{1}^{n}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{d^{n}}^{n}>\lambda_{d^{n}+1}^{n}=\ldots=\lambda_{m}\geq 0.

If
\{\uppsi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
solves (
3.6.27
) then
\{\psi_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
is a solution to (
3.6.26
) with the representation (
3.5.13
). On the other hand, if
\{\psi_{i}^{h}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
is a solution to (
3.6.26
) then we infer from
\psi_{i}^{n}\in V^{h}
the existence of a uniquely determined matrix
{\bm{\Psi}}=[\uppsi_{1}\,|\ldots|\,\uppsi_{\ell}]\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times\ell}
satisfying (
3.5.13
). It follows that the columns
\{\uppsi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of
{\bm{\Psi}}
solve (
3.6.27
). Finally, we can quantify the POD approximation error as follows

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\Big\|y_{j}^{h}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y_{j}^{h},\psi_{i}^{n}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}^{n}\Big\|_{X}^{2}=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\lambda_{i}^{n}.

(3.6.28)

POD Galerkin scheme

Suppose that we have computed a POD basis
\{\psi_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell
. Again, we set

\displaystyle X^{n\ell}=\Span\,\big\{\psi_{1}^{n},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}^{n}\big\}\subset V^{h}\subset X

and

\displaystyle H^{n\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\psi_{1}^{nH},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}^{nH}\big\},\quad V^{n\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\psi_{1}^{nV},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}^{nV}\big\}

for the choices
X=H
and
X=V
, respectively. To simplify the presentation we utilize the same time grid
\{t_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
for (
3.6.20
), (
3.6.26
) and the POD Galerkin scheme introduced below in (
3.6.29
). Next we replace (
3.6.20
) by the following POD Galerkin scheme: find
\{y^{h\ell}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset X^{h\ell}
satisfying

{\langle\overline{\partial}y^{h\ell}_{j},\psi\rangle}_{H}+a(y^{h\ell}_{j},\psi)+{\langle\mathcal{C}y_{j}^{h\ell}+\mathcal{N}(y^{h\ell}_{j}),\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}={\langle g_{j}(u),\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

(3.6.29a)

for all
\psi\in X^{n\ell}
,
2\leq j\leq n
and

y^{h\ell}_{1}=\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}y_{\circ}

(3.6.29b)

with
\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}_{H}
if
X=H
or with
\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{n\ell}_{H}
if
X=V
. In (
3.6.29a
) we set
\overline{\partial}y^{h\ell}_{j}=(y^{h\ell}_{j}-y^{h\ell}_{j-1})/\delta t_{j}
. From
y_{j}^{h\ell}\in X^{h\ell}
we infer that there are uniquely determined coefficient vectors
\mathrm{y}_{j}^{h\ell}=(\mathrm{y}_{ji}^{h\ell})_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
satisfying

y_{j}^{h\ell}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{y}_{ji}^{h\ell}\psi_{i}^{n}\quad\text{for }j=1,\ldots,n.

(3.6.30)

Inserting (
3.6.30
) into (
3.6.29
), choosing
\psi=\psi_{i}^{n}
,
1\leq i\leq\ell
, and using the notation introduced in Section
3.4.4
we obtain

\displaystyle\big({\bm{M}}^{h\ell}+\delta t_{j}{\bm{A}}^{h\ell}\big)\mathrm{y}_{j}^{h\ell}+\mathrm{n}^{h\ell}(\mathrm{y}^{h\ell}_{j})

\displaystyle={\bm{M}}^{h\ell}\mathrm{y}^{h\ell}_{j-1}+\delta t_{j}\mathrm{g}_{j}^{h\ell}(u)\text{ for }2\leq j\leq n,

(3.6.31a)

\displaystyle{\bm{M}}^{h\ell}\mathrm{y}^{h}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{y}_{\circ}^{h\ell}

(3.6.31b)

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{n}^{h\ell}(\mathrm{v})=\bigg(\Big\langle\mathcal{N}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{v}_{j}\psi_{j}^{n}\Big),\psi_{i}^{n}\Big\rangle\bigg)_{1\leq i\leq\ell}

for the vector
\mathrm{v}=(\mathrm{v}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq\ell}
. In the following proposition, existence and a-priori estimates for the solution
\{y^{h\ell}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
are established. The proof follows by the same arguments as in the proof of Proposition
3.6.10
.

Proposition 3.6.11
.

Let Assumption
3.6.4
hold. The POD basis
\{\psi_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
is computed by solving (
3.6.26
). If the time step
\delta t
is sufficiently small, there exists a unique sequence
\{y^{h\ell}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset X^{n\ell}
to (
3.6.29
). Moreover, the following estimates are satisfied:

\displaystyle{\|y_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}\leq e^{-C_{1}(j-1)\delta t}\bigg({\|\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\sum_{l=2}^{j}\delta t_{l}\,{\|g_{l}(u)\|}_{V^{\prime}}^{2}\bigg)

for
j=2,\ldots,n
with the constant
C_{1}=2\gamma_{1}/c_{V}^{2}
and

\displaystyle{\|y_{n}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+\sum_{j=2}^{n}{\|y_{j}^{h\ell}-y_{j-1}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+\gamma_{1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta t_{j}\,{\|y_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}\leq{\|\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\sum_{j=2}^{n}\delta t_{j}\,{\|g_{j}(u)\|}_{V^{\prime}}^{2}.

POD a-priori error analysis

Let
\{y^{h}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
and
\{y^{h\ell}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
be solutions to (
3.6.20
) and (
3.6.29
), respectively. Our goal is to derive an a-priori error estimate for the term

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|y_{j}^{h}-y_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}.

Here we utilize the results presented in Section
2.4.2
and proceed similarly as in Section
3.5.5
.

In the following assumption we suppose that all hypotheses of Propositions
3.6.10
and
3.6.11
are satisfied so that there exist unique solutions
\{y^{h}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
and
\{y^{h\ell}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
to (
3.6.20
) and (
3.6.29
), respectively.

Assumption 3.6.12
.

1)

Suppose that Assumption
3.6.4
hold.

2)

There is a constant
\zeta>0
which is independent of
n
, such that
\Delta t/\delta t\leq\zeta
.

3)

Let the POD basis
\{\psi_{i}^{n}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell
be computed from (
3.6.26
).

4)

Assume that
\delta t
is sufficiently small so that there exist unique solutions
\{y^{h}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
and
\{y^{h\ell}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
to (
3.6.20
) and (
3.6.29
). Moreover, the sequence
\{y^{h}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
satisfies

\max_{1\leq j\leq n}{\|y_{j}^{h}\|}_{V}\leq C

(3.6.32)

with a constant
C>0
which does not depend on
n
and
h
.

The proof of the next theorem is given in Section
3.8.5
.

Theorem 3.6.13
.

Suppose that Assumption
3.6.12
hold. Then

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|y_{j}^{h}-y_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}\leq C\cdot\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\lambda_{i}^{nH}\,{\|\psi_{i}^{nH}\|}_{V}^{2}&&X=H,~\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}_{H},\\
&\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d^{n}}\lambda_{i}^{nV}\,{\|\psi_{i}^{nV}-\mathcal{Q}^{h\ell}_{H}\psi_{i}^{nV}\|}_{V}^{2}&&X=V,~\mathcal{P}^{n\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{n\ell}_{H}\end{aligned}\right.

(3.6.33)

with a constant
C>0
that is independent of
n
,
h
and
\ell
.

POD a-posteriori error analysis

Let Assumption
3.6.4
hold. We suppose that
\{y_{j}^{h}\}_{j=1}^{n}
and
\{y_{j}^{h\ell}\}_{j=1}^{n}
are solutions to (
3.6.20
) and (
3.6.29
), respectively. To estimate the error between the (unknown)
y_{j}^{h}
and the POD solution
y_{j}^{h}
for
j=1,\ldots,n
we derive an a-posteriori error estimate in the next theorem which is proved in Section
3.8.5
. This extends our results for the linear case presented in Section
3.5.6
.

Theorem 3.6.14
.

Suppose that Assumption
3.6.12
hold. Then the a-priori error estimate

\displaystyle{\|y_{j}^{h}-y^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}\leq\big(1+\hat{c}\Delta t\big)^{j-1}{\|(\mathcal{P}^{h}-\mathcal{P}^{n\ell})y_{\circ}\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\sum_{l=2}^{j}\big(1+\hat{c}\Delta t\big)^{j+1-l}\delta t_{l}\,{\|r_{l}^{h\ell}\|}_{(V^{h})^{\prime}}^{2}

for the constant
\hat{c}=2c_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}C^{2}/\gamma_{1}
and the residual

\displaystyle r_{j}^{h\ell}=-\left({\langle\overline{\partial}y_{j}^{h\ell}+\mathcal{C}y_{j}^{h\ell}+\mathcal{N}(y_{j}^{h\ell})-g_{j}(u),\cdot\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}+a(y_{j}^{h\ell},\cdot)\right)\in(V^{h})^{\prime}.

3.6.5
The empirical interpolation method

The ROM introduced in (
3.6.17
) is a nonlinear system. Hence, the problem with the POD Galerkin approach is the complexity of the evaluation of the nonlinearity
\mathrm{n}^{h\ell}
in (
3.6.17
). Setting
{\bm{\Psi}}=((\Psi_{ij}))\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times\ell}
with
{\bm{\Psi}}=[\uppsi_{1}\,|\ldots\,\uppsi_{\ell}]\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times\ell}
we derive for any
\mathrm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
that

\displaystyle\big(\mathrm{n}^{h\ell}(\mathrm{y})\big)_{i}

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle\mathcal{N}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{y}_{j}\psi_{j}^{h}\Big),\psi_{i}\bigg\rangle_{V^{\prime},V}

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle\mathcal{N}\bigg(\sum_{l=1}^{m}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\Psi_{lj}\mathrm{y}_{j}\Big)\varphi_{l}\bigg),\sum_{k=1}^{m}\Psi_{ki}\varphi_{k}\bigg\rangle_{V^{\prime},V}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\Psi_{ki}\,\bigg\langle\mathcal{N}\bigg(\sum_{l=1}^{m}({\bm{\Psi}}\mathrm{y})_{l}\varphi_{l}\bigg),\varphi_{k}\bigg\rangle_{V^{\prime},V}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}({\bm{\Psi}}^{\top})_{ik}\,\big(\mathrm{n}^{h}({\bm{\Psi}}\mathrm{y})\big)_{k}=\big({\bm{\Psi}}^{\top}\mathrm{n}^{h}({\bm{\Psi}}\mathrm{y})\big)_{i}.

This implies

\displaystyle\mathrm{n}^{h\ell}(\mathrm{y})={\bm{\Psi}}^{\top}\mathrm{n}^{h}({\bm{\Psi}}\mathrm{y})\quad\text{for any }\mathrm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}.

This can be interpreted in the way that the vector
\mathrm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
is first expanded to a vector
{\bm{\Psi}}\mathrm{y}
of dimension
m
, then the nonlinearity
\mathrm{n}^{h}({\bm{\Psi}}\mathrm{y})
is evaluated and at last the result is reduced back to the low dimension
\ell
of the reduced-order model. This is computationally expensive. Further this means that our reduced-order model is not independent of the full dimension
m
. Note that when applying a Newton method to the system (
3.6.17
) the Jacobian of the nonlinearity is also needed. For any
\mathrm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
and
i,\nu,\in\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathrm{y}_{\nu}}\,\big(\mathrm{n}^{h\ell}(\mathrm{y})\big)_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathrm{y}_{\nu}}\,\Bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{m}\Psi_{ki}\,\bigg\langle\mathcal{N}\bigg(\sum_{l=1}^{m}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\Psi_{lj}\mathrm{y}_{j}\Big)\varphi_{l}\bigg),\varphi_{k}\bigg\rangle_{V^{\prime},V}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\Psi_{ki}\,\bigg\langle\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\bigg(\sum_{l=1}^{m}({\bm{\Psi}}\mathrm{y})_{l}\varphi_{l}\bigg)\sum_{\mu=1}^{m}\Psi_{l\mu}\varphi_{\mu},\varphi_{k}\bigg\rangle_{V^{\prime},V}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\Psi_{ki}\sum_{\mu=1}^{m}\Psi_{l\mu}\,\bigg\langle\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\bigg(\sum_{l=1}^{m}({\bm{\Psi}}\mathrm{y})_{l}\varphi_{l}\bigg)\varphi_{\mu},\varphi_{k}\bigg\rangle_{V^{\prime},V}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\nabla\mathrm{n}^{h\ell}(\mathrm{y})={\bm{\Psi}}^{\top}{\bm{D}}^{h}({\bm{\Psi}}\mathrm{y}){\bm{\Psi}}\quad\text{for any }\mathrm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}

with

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}^{h}({\bm{\Psi}}\mathrm{y})=\bigg(\Big\langle\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{m}({\bm{\Psi}}\mathrm{y})_{k}\varphi_{k}\Big)\varphi_{j},\varphi_{i}\Big\rangle_{V^{\prime},V}\bigg)_{1\leq i,j\leq m}.

Again the same problem can be observed. Note that here the computation expenses are larger since the Jacobians are of dimension
m\times m
. Hence not only a vector is transformed but a matrix of full dimension. To avoid this computational expensive evaluation, the
empirical interpolation method (EIM)
was introduced
[
BNP04
]
. This method is often used in combination with the reduced basis approach
[
Gre12
]
. The second approach we will investigate here is the
discrete empirical interpolation method
(DEIM) as introduced in
[
CS10a
,
CS10b
,
CS12
]
. While the EIM implementation is based on a greedy algorithm, the DEIM strategy is based on a POD approach combined with a greedy algorithm. We will now discuss both methods. We define

\displaystyle\mathrm{b}^{h}=\mathrm{n}^{h}({\bm{\Psi}}\mathrm{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\quad\text{for }\mathrm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}.

Now
\mathrm{b}^{h}
is approximated by a Galerkin ansatz utilizing
\mathsf{p}
linearly independent functions
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{\mathsf{p}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, i.e.

\mathrm{b}^{h}\approx\sum_{k=1}^{\mathsf{p}}\mathrm{c}_{k}\phi_{k}=\Phi\mathrm{c}

(3.6.34)

with
\mathrm{c}=[\mathrm{c}_{1}\,|\ldots|\,\mathrm{c}_{\mathsf{p}}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{p}}
and
\Phi=[\phi_{1}|\ldots|\phi_{\mathsf{p}}]\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times\mathsf{p}}
. Hence we can write the approximation of
\mathrm{n}^{h\ell}
as

\displaystyle\mathrm{n}^{h\ell}(\mathrm{y})=\Psi^{\top}\mathrm{n}^{h}(\Psi\mathrm{y})=\Psi^{\top}\mathrm{b}^{h}\approx\Psi^{\top}\Phi\mathrm{c}.

The question arising is how to compute the matrix
{\bm{\Phi}}
and the vector
\mathrm{c}
. Let
\vec{\imath}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{p}}
be an index vector and
{\bm{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times\mathsf{p}}
a given matrix. Then by
{\bm{B}}_{\vec{\imath}}
we denote the submatrix consisting of the rows of
{\bm{B}}
corresponding to the indices in
\vec{\imath}
. Obviously, if we choose
\mathsf{p}
indices then the overdetermined system
\mathrm{b}^{h}={\bm{\Phi}}\mathrm{c}
can be solved by choosing
\mathsf{p}
rows of
\mathrm{b}^{h}
and
{\bm{\Phi}}
. Here it is assumed that the submatrix
\Phi_{\vec{\imath}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{p}\times\mathsf{p}}
is invertible.

Assume we have computed
{\bm{\Phi}}
and
\vec{\imath}
by an algorithm. Then we proceed as follows. For simplicity we introduce here the matrix
{\bm{P}}=[e_{\vec{\imath}_{1}}\,|\ldots|\,e_{\vec{\imath}_{\mathsf{p}}}]\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times\mathsf{p}}
, where
e_{\vec{\imath}_{i}}=(0,\ldots,0,1,0,\ldots,0)^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a vector with all zeros and at the
\vec{\imath}_{i}
-th row a one. Note that
{\bm{\Phi}}_{\vec{\imath}}={\bm{P}}^{\top}{\bm{\Phi}}
holds. To evaluate the approximate nonlinearity we need
\mathrm{c}
. Since we know
{\bm{\Phi}}
and the index vector
\vec{\imath}
, we can compute

\displaystyle\mathrm{c}=({\bm{P}}^{\top}{\bm{\Phi}})^{-1}{\bm{P}}^{\top}\mathrm{b}^{h}=\left({\bm{P}}^{\top}{\bm{\Phi}}\right)^{-1}{\bm{P}}^{\top}\mathrm{n}^{h}({\bm{\Psi}}\mathrm{y})\quad\text{for }\mathrm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}.

Suppose that the matrix
\mathrm{P}
can be moved into the nonlinearity. Then we obtain

\displaystyle{\bm{P}}^{\top}\mathrm{n}^{h}({\bm{\Psi}}\mathrm{y})=\big(\mathrm{n}^{h}({\bm{\Psi}}\mathrm{y})\big)_{\vec{\imath}}=\tilde{\mathrm{n}}^{h}({\bm{P}}^{\top}{\bm{\Psi}}\mathrm{y})\quad\text{for }\mathrm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell},

where
\tilde{\mathrm{n}}^{h}:\mathbb{R}^{|\vec{\imath}|}\to\mathbb{R}^{|\vec{\imath}|}
only depends on the vector
({\bm{\Psi}}\mathrm{y})_{\vec{\imath}}
. An extension for general nonlinearities is shown in
[
CS10b
]
. Let us now have a look at the computational expenses. The matrices
{\bm{P}}^{\top}{\bm{\Psi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{p}\times\ell}
,
{\bm{P}}^{\top}{\bm{\Phi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{p}\times\mathsf{p}}
and
{\bm{\Psi}}^{\top}{\bm{\Phi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell\times\mathsf{p}}
can be precomputed. All these are independent of the full dimension
m
. Additionally, during the iterations the nonlinearity only has to be evaluated at the interpolation points, i.e. only at
\mathsf{p}
points. This allows the reduced-order model to be completely independent of the full dimension. Note that the used method is an interpolation and therefore is exact at the interpolation points. For the Jacobian the approach is similar.

Let us now turn to the EIM and DEIM algorithms. When (
3.6.13
) is solved, the nonlinearity
\mathrm{n}^{h}(\mathrm{y}^{h}(t))
is evaluated for each time step
t_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,n
. If these evaluations are stored, the procedure to determine
{\bm{\Phi}}
and the index vector
\vec{\imath}
does not involve any further evaluations of the nonlinearity. We denote by
{\bm{F}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
the matrix with columns
\mathrm{n}^{h}(\mathrm{y}^{h}(t_{j}))\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
for
j=1,\ldots,n
. Next let us have a look at the two algorithms of interest and let us present some numerical results. In the algorithms
|\cdot|_{\infty}
stands for the maximum norm in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and the operation ‘
\argmax
’ returns the index, where
the maximum entry occurs. In Algorithm
3.6.5
we state the empirical interpolation method (EIM) using a greedy algorithm.

0:

\mathsf{p}
and matrix
\mathrm{F}=[\mathrm{n}^{h}(\mathrm{y}^{h}(t_{1}))\,|\ldots|\,\mathrm{n}^{h}(\mathrm{y}^{h}(t_{n}))]\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
;

1:

k\leftarrow\argmax_{j=1,\ldots,n}|f(t_{j},y(t_{j}))|_{\infty}
;

2:

\xi\leftarrow\mathrm{n}^{h}(\mathrm{y}^{h}(t_{k}))
;

3:

idx
\leftarrow\argmax_{j=1,\ldots,m}|\xi_{j}|
;

4:

\phi_{1}\leftarrow\xi/\xi_{\{\text{idx}\}}
;

5:

{\bm{\Phi}}=[\phi^{1}]
and
\vec{\imath}=
idx;

6:

for

i=2
to
\ell^{EI}

do

7:

Solve
{\bm{\Phi}}_{\{\text{idx}\}}\mathrm{c}_{j}=\mathrm{n}^{h}(\mathrm{y}^{h}(t_{j}))_{\{\text{idx}\}}
for
j=1,\ldots,n
;

8:

k\leftarrow\argmax_{j=1,\ldots,n}|\mathrm{n}^{h}(\mathrm{y}(t_{j}))-{\bm{\Phi}}\mathrm{c}_{j}|_{\infty}
;

9:

\xi\leftarrow\mathrm{n}^{h}(\mathrm{y}^{h}(t_{k}))
;

10:

idx
\leftarrow\argmax_{j=1,\ldots,m}|(\xi-{\bm{\Phi}}\mathrm{c}_{k})_{\{j\}}|
;

11:

\phi^{i}\leftarrow(\xi-{\bm{\Phi}}\mathrm{c}_{k})/(\xi-{\bm{\Phi}}\mathrm{c}_{k})_{\{\text{idx}\}}
;

12:

{\bm{\Phi}}\leftarrow[{\bm{\Phi}}|\phi^{i}]
and
\vec{\imath}\leftarrow[\vec{\imath}\,|\,
idx
]
;

13:

end

for

14:

return

{\bm{\Phi}}
and
\vec{\imath}

Note that the basis
\phi^{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,\mathsf{p}
, is chosen from the provided snapshots of
\mathrm{n}^{h}(\mathrm{y}^{h}(t))
by scaling and shifting. The obtained basis is not orthonormal. The advantage of this method is that the submatrix
{\bm{\Phi}}_{\vec{\imath}}
is an upper triangular matrix. Hence solving for
\mathrm{c}(t)
is computationally cheap. The drawback of this method is that the computation of the basis is more expensive than the discrete empirical interpolation method (DEIM) presented in Algorithm
3.6.5
.

0:

\mathsf{p}
and matrix
{\bm{F}}=[\mathrm{n}^{h}(\mathrm{y}^{h}(t_{1}))\,|\ldots|\,\mathrm{n}^{h}(\mathrm{y}^{h}(t_{n}))]\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
;

1:

Compute POD basis
{\bm{\Phi}}=[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{\mathsf{p}}]
for
{\bm{F}}
;

2:

idx
\leftarrow\argmax_{j=1,\ldots,m}|(\phi_{1})_{\{j\}}|
;

3:

{\bm{U}}=[\phi_{1}]
and
\vec{\imath}=
idx;

4:

for

i=2
to
\mathsf{p}

do

5:

u\leftarrow\phi_{i}
;

6:

Solve
{\bm{U}}_{\vec{\imath}}\,\mathrm{c}=\mathrm{u}_{\vec{\imath}}
;

7:

\mathrm{r}\leftarrow\mathrm{u}-{\bm{U}}\mathrm{c}
;

8:

idx
\leftarrow\argmax_{j=1,\ldots,m}|(\mathrm{r})_{\{j\}}|
;

9:

{\bm{U}}\leftarrow[{\bm{U}}|\mathrm{u}]
and
\vec{\imath}\leftarrow[\vec{\imath}\,|\,
idx
]
;

10:

end

for

11:

return

{\bm{\Phi}}
and
\vec{\imath}

The DEIM algorithm on the other hand generates the basis using the POD approach. Here the previously introduced POD approach is applied to the snapshots of the nonlinearity
\mathrm{b}^{n}(t)=\mathrm{n}^{h}(\mathrm{y}^{h}(t))
to compute
{\bm{\Phi}}
. The matrix
{\bm{\Phi}}_{\vec{\imath}}
obtained by the DEIM method has no special structure. Hence evaluating the nonlinearity using DEIM is more expensive compared to EIM. The computational cost can be reduced by precomputing a LU decomposition of
{\bm{\Phi}}_{\vec{\imath}}
. Then the evaluation of the nonlinearity using DEIM involves two solves compared
to one solve for the EIM. Further when comparing the two algorithms it can be seen that the computation for the EIM basis is more expensive compared to the DEIM basis. This can be seen when comparing line 7 in Algorithm
3.6.5
and line 6 in Algorithm
3.6.5
. In each iteration of Algorithm
3.6.5
one has to solve
n
linear systems compared to one linear system in Algorithm
3.6.5
. The selection for the interpolation points in both algorithms is similar and is based on a greedy algorithm. The idea is to successively select spatial points to limit the growth of an error bound. The indices are constructed inductively from the input data. For more details we refer the reader to
[
BNP04
,
CS10a
]
.

3.7
The POD method for elliptic problems

In Section
2.2
we have introduced the continuous POD method an a closed and bounded parameter set
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{p}}
with
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{N}
. For the choice
\mathscr{D}=[0,T]
we utilized the POD method to derive Galerkin POD schemes for evolution equations in Section
3.3
. In this section we choose a finite-dimensional set
\mathscr{D}
containing parameters which enter parametrized elliptic variational problems. Then the computed POD bases are used to derive reduced-order parametrized systems.

Since we focus on evolution problems in this book, we only discuss briefly the application of the POD method to certain parametrized elliptic variational problems. For more details we refer to
[
KV07
,
KV09
,
KV12
,
VH08
]
, for instance. Let us mention that especially in the context of elliptic variational problems the
reduced basis method
offers an efficient alternative for the derivation of low-order models; see, e.g., in the textbooks
[
HRS16
,
PR05
,
QMN16
]
.

3.7.1
The parametrized elliptic variational problem

Let
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{p}}
be a bounded and closed set. Suppose that for
{\bm{\mu}}\in D
the
parameter-dependent bilinear form

a({\bm{\mu}};\cdot\,,\cdot):V\times V\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies

\displaystyle\big|a({\bm{\mu}};\varphi,\phi)\big|\leq\gamma\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}{\|\phi\|}_{V}

\displaystyle\text{for all }\varphi,\phi\in V\text{ and for }{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathscr{D},

(3.7.1a)

\displaystyle a({\bm{\mu}};\varphi,\varphi)\geq\gamma_{1}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}^{2}

\displaystyle\text{for all }\varphi\in V\text{ and for }{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathscr{D}

(3.7.1b)

for positive constants
\gamma
,
\gamma_{1}
. Further, let
\mathcal{F}({\bm{\mu}})\in V^{\prime}
for
{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathscr{D}
be a parameter-dependent right-hand side. For given
\mu\in\mathscr{D}
, we consider the variational problem: find
y=y({\bm{\mu}})\in V
solving

a({\bm{\mu}};y({\bm{\mu}}),\varphi)={\langle\mathcal{F}({\bm{\mu}}),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in V.

(3.7.2)

Example 3.7.1
.

For
{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{a}},{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{b}}\in\mathbb{R}
with
{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{a}}\leq{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{b}}
we define the parameter set
\mathscr{D}=[\mu_{\mathsf{a}},\mu_{\mathsf{b}}]
. Then we understand the parameter dependent bilinear form
a({\bm{\mu}};\cdot\,,\cdot):V\times V\to\mathbb{R}
as

\displaystyle a({\bm{\mu}};\varphi,\phi)={\langle\varphi,\phi\rangle}_{V}+{\bm{\mu}}\,{\langle\varphi,\phi\rangle}_{H}\quad\text{for }\varphi,\phi\in V\text{ and }{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathscr{D}.

For any
{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathscr{D}
we infer from the embedding inequality (
2.4.1
) that

\displaystyle|a({\bm{\mu}};\varphi,\phi)|\leq\left(1+c_{V}^{2}\,\max\{|{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{a}}|,|{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{b}}|\}\right){\|\varphi\|}_{V}{\|\phi\|}_{V}\quad\text{for all }\varphi,\phi\in V,

i.e., the bilinear form
a({\bm{\mu}};\cdot\,,\cdot)
satisfies (
3.7.1a
) with
\gamma=1+\max\{|{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{a}}|,|{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{b}}|\}c_{V}^{2}
. Further

\displaystyle a(\mu;\varphi,\varphi)={\|\varphi\|}_{V}^{2}+\mu\,{\|\varphi\|}_{H}^{2}\geq{\|\varphi\|}_{V}^{2}+\mu_{a}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{H}^{2}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in V\text{ and }\mu\in\mathscr{D}.

If
{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{a}}\geq 0
holds, then (
3.7.1b
) is satisfied with
\gamma_{1}=1
. In the case
{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{a}}<0
we infer from (
2.4.1
) that

\displaystyle a({\bm{\mu}};\varphi,\varphi)\geq{\|\varphi\|}_{V}^{2}+{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{a}}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{H}^{2}\geq\big(1+{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{a}}c_{V}^{2}\big){\|\varphi\|}_{V}^{2}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in V\text{ and }\mu\in\mathscr{D}.

Summarizing, (
3.7.1b
) holds if
\gamma_{1}=1+\min\{0,{\bm{\mu}}_{\mathsf{a}}c_{V}^{2}\}>0
is fulfilled.
\blacklozenge

The following theorem ensures that (
3.7.2
) admits a unique solution. Its proof is given in Section
3.8.6
.

Theorem 3.7.2
.

Suppose that the parameter dependent bilinear form
a({\bm{\mu}};\cdot\,,\cdot)
satisfies (
3.7.1
) and
\mathcal{F}({\bm{\mu}})\in V^{\prime}
holds true for any
{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathscr{D}
. Then there exists a unique solution
y({\bm{\mu}})\in V
to (
3.7.2
) for every
{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathscr{D}
. Moreover, we have

{\|y({\bm{\mu}})\|}_{V}\leq\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\,{\|\mathcal{F}({\bm{\mu}})\|}_{V^{\prime}}\quad\text{for every }{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathscr{D}.

(3.7.3)

In particular, if the mapping
{\bm{\mu}}\mapsto\mathcal{F}({\bm{\mu}})\in V^{\prime}
belongs to
L^{2}(\mathscr{D};V^{\prime})
, the mapping
{\bm{\mu}}\mapsto y({\bm{\mu}})
belongs to
L^{2}(\mathscr{D};V)
.

3.7.2
The continuous POD method

Together with (
3.7.2
) we will consider a discretized variational problem, where we apply POD for the discretization of
V
. For that purpose let
y({\bm{\mu}})\in V
be the associated solution to (
3.7.2
) for a chosen parameter
{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathscr{D}
. We suppose that
\mathcal{F}\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D};V^{\prime})
holds, so that
y\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D};V)\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\mathscr{D};H)
by Theorem
3.7.2
. Next we apply a continuous variant of the POD method. Further,
X
denotes either the space
V
or the space
H
. We quickly summarize the results from Section
2.2
for the current situation. Let us define the bounded linear operator
\mathcal{Y}:L^{2}(\mathscr{D})\to X

by

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}\phi=\int_{\mathscr{D}}\phi({\bm{\mu}})y({\bm{\mu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}\quad\text{for }\phi\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D}).

Then the snapshot space
\mathscr{V}
is given as

\displaystyle\mathscr{V}=\mathrm{ran}\,\mathcal{Y}\subset V.

Let
d=\dim\mathscr{V}\leq\infty
. The Hilbert space adjoint
\mathcal{Y}^{\star}:X\to L^{2}(\mathscr{D})
of
\mathcal{Y}
is given by

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{Y}^{\star}\psi\big)({\bm{\mu}})={\langle\psi,y({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{X}\quad\text{for }\psi\in X\text{ and }{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathscr{D}.

Furthermore, we find that the bounded, linear, symmetric and nonnegative operator
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\star}:X\to X
has the form

\mathcal{R}\psi=\int_{\mathscr{D}}{\langle\psi,y({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{X}y({\bm{\mu}})\,\mathrm{d}\mu\quad\text{for }\psi\in X.

(3.7.4)

The operator
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{Y}^{\star}\mathcal{Y}:L^{2}(\mathscr{D})\to L^{2}(\mathscr{D})
is given by

\big(\mathcal{K}\phi\big)({\bm{\mu}})=\int_{\mathscr{D}}{\langle y({\bm{\nu}}),y({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{X}\phi({\bm{\nu}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}\quad\text{for }\phi\in L^{2}(\mathscr{D}).

(3.7.5)

Since the mapping
{\bm{\mu}}\mapsto y({\bm{\mu}})\in V
is in
L^{2}(\mathscr{D};V)
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathscr{D}}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\big|{\langle y({\bm{\nu}}),y({\bm{\mu}})\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\nu}}\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}<\infty.

From
[
Yos95
, pp. 197 and 277]
it follows that
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{Y}^{\star}\mathcal{Y}
is compact and, therefore,
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}^{\star}
is
compact as well. From the Theorems
A.3.49
and
A.3.50
it follows that there exists a complete orthonormal basis
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
of
V
and a sequence
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
of nonnegative real numbers so that for any
\ell\leq d

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\psi_{i}=\lambda_{i}\psi_{i}\text{ for }1\leq i\leq\ell,\quad\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{\ell}>0.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathscr{D}}{\|y({\bm{\mu}})\|}_{X}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mu}}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\lambda_{i}.

Remark 3.7.3

(Methods of snapshots)
.

Analogous to Remark
2.2.9
, we find that the bounded, linear, symmetric and nonnegative operator
\mathcal{K}
defined in (
3.7.5
) has the same eigenvalues
