\displaystyle={\langle\mathcal{N}(y_{j}^{h\ell})-\mathcal{N}(y_{j}^{h}),\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}-a(\varrho^{h\ell}_{j},\psi)-{\langle\mathcal{C}\varrho^{h\ell}_{j},\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

for
j=2,\ldots,n
. Choosing
\psi=\vartheta^{h\ell}_{j}\in X^{n\ell}
and utilizing Lemma
A.2.41
and (
3.6.3
) we derive from (
3.8.74
) that

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}-{\|\vartheta_{j-1}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}-\vartheta_{j-1}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+2\gamma_{1}\delta t_{j}\,{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}

(3.8.75)

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta t_{j}\big(\gamma\,{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}+{\|\mathcal{C}\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V^{\prime}}{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}\big)+2\delta t_{j}\,\big|{\langle\mathcal{N}(y_{j}^{h\ell})-\mathcal{N}(y_{j}^{h}),\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\big|

for
j=2,\ldots,n
. Applying Young’s inequality it follows that

\gamma\,{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}\leq\frac{\gamma_{1}}{5}\,{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}+c_{1}\,{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}

(3.8.76)

for a non-negative constant
c_{1}
depending on
\gamma
,
\gamma_{1}
and

{\|\mathcal{C}\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V^{\prime}}{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}\leq{\|\mathcal{C}\|}_{\mathscr{L}(V,V^{\prime})}{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}\leq\frac{\gamma_{1}}{5}\,{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}+c_{2}\,{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}

(3.8.77)

for a constant
c_{2}\geq 0
depending on
\gamma_{1}
and
\|\mathcal{C}\|_{\mathscr{L}(V,V^{\prime})}
. We proceed by estimating the nonlinear terms on the right-hand side of (
3.8.75
). Note that

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(y_{j}^{h\ell})-\mathcal{N}(y_{j}^{h})=\mathcal{M}(y_{j}^{h},y_{j}^{h\ell}-y_{j}^{h})+\mathcal{M}(y_{j}^{h\ell}-y_{j}^{h})+\mathcal{M}(y_{j}^{h\ell}-y_{j}^{h},y_{j}^{h}).

By Assumption
3.6.12
-4) there exists a constant
c_{3}=C
which is independent of
h
and
n
such that (
3.6.32
) holds. Using (
3.6.5
), (
2.4.1
), Young’s inequality and (
3.6.32
) we conclude that there are two non-negative constants
c_{4}
,
c_{5}
, which depend on
\gamma_{1}
,
c_{V}
,
c_{3}
,
c_{\mathcal{N}}
and
\delta_{3}
, so that

\displaystyle\big|{\langle\mathcal{M}(y_{j}^{h},y_{j}^{h\ell}-y_{j}^{h}),\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\big|

(3.8.78)

\displaystyle=\big|{\langle\mathcal{M}(y_{j}^{h},\varrho_{j}^{h\ell})+\mathcal{M}(y_{j}^{h},\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}),\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\big|=\big|{\langle\mathcal{M}(y_{j}^{h},\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}),\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\big|

\displaystyle\leq c_{\mathcal{N}}c_{V}^{\delta_{3}}\,{\|y_{j}^{h}\|}_{V}{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{1-\delta_{3}}{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{\delta_{3}}\leq\frac{\gamma_{1}}{5}\,{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}+c_{4}\,{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+c_{5}\,{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}.

Again applying (
3.6.5
), (
3.6.32
), (
2.4.1
) and Young’s inequality we infer that there exist two non-negative constants
c_{6}
,
c_{7}
, which depend on
\gamma_{1}
,
c_{V}
,
c_{3}
,
c_{\mathcal{N}}
and
\delta_{3}
, satisfying

\displaystyle\big|{\langle\mathcal{M}(y_{j}^{h\ell}-y_{j}^{h},y_{j}^{h}),\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\big|

\displaystyle=\big|{\langle\mathcal{M}(\varrho_{j}^{h\ell},y_{j}^{h})+\mathcal{M}(\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell},y_{j}^{h}),\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\big|

(3.8.79)

\displaystyle\leq c_{\mathcal{M}}\,{\|y_{j}^{h}\|}_{V}\Big({\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}+c_{V}^{\delta_{3}}\,{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{1-\delta_{3}}{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{\delta_{3}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\gamma_{1}}{5}{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}+c_{6}\,{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+c_{7}\,{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}.

By (
3.6.5
) we get

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{N}(y_{j}^{h\ell}-y_{j}^{h}),\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}={\langle\mathcal{M}(\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell},\varrho_{j}^{h\ell})+\mathcal{N}(\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}),\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}.

From (
3.6.32
) it follows that there is a non-negative constant
c_{8}
such that

\max_{1\leq j\leq n}\Big({\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{\delta_{3}}{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{1-\delta_{3}},{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}\Big)\leq c_{8}.

(3.8.80)

Using (
3.6.5
), (
3.8.80
) and Young’s inequality we get

\displaystyle\big|{\langle\mathcal{N}(y_{j}^{h\ell}-y_{j}^{h}),\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\big|

\displaystyle\leq c_{\mathcal{M}}c_{8}\Big({\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}+{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{1-\delta_{3}}{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{\delta_{3}}\Big)

(3.8.81)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\gamma_{1}}{5}{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}+c_{9}\,{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+c_{10}\,{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}

for non-negative constants
c_{9}
and
c_{10}
depending on
\gamma_{1}
,
c_{1}
,
c_{\mathcal{M}}
and
\delta_{3}
. Combining (
3.8.75
), (
3.8.77
), (
3.8.78
), (
3.8.79
), (
3.8.81
) we deduce the estimate

{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}-{\|\vartheta_{j-1}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+\gamma_{1}\delta t_{j}\,{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}\leq\delta t_{j}\big(c_{11}\,{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+c_{12}\,{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}\big)

(3.8.82)

with
c_{11}=2(c_{4}+c_{6}+c_{9})
and
c_{12}=2(c_{1}+c_{2}+c_{5}+c_{7}+c_{10})
. Consequently,

\displaystyle{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}\leq{\|\vartheta_{j-1}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+\delta t_{j}\big(c_{11}\,{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+c_{12}\,{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}\big)

Suppose that

\Delta t\leq\frac{1}{2c_{11}}.

(3.8.83)

From (
3.8.83
) we have
1/2\leq 1-c_{11}\delta t_{j}<1
and

\frac{1}{1-c_{11}\delta t_{j}}\leq\frac{1}{1-c_{11}\Delta t}\leq 1+2c_{11}\Delta t

(3.8.84)

for
j=2,\ldots,n
. From (
3.8.83
), (
3.8.84
) and by assumption we infer that

\displaystyle{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1+2c_{11}\Delta t)\Big({\|\vartheta_{j-1}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+c_{12}\delta t_{j}\,{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}\Big)

(3.8.85)

\displaystyle\leq\big(1+2c_{11}\Delta t\big)^{j-1}\,{\|\vartheta_{1}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+c_{12}\sum_{l=2}^{j}\delta t_{j}\big(1+2c_{11}\Delta t\big)^{j+1-l}\,{\|\varrho_{l}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}

for
j=2,\ldots,n
. Due to Assumption
3.5.1
-3) the quotient
\Delta t/\delta t
is uniformly bounded by
\zeta
. Thus
0\leq 2c_{11}\Delta t\leq c_{13}\delta t
with
c_{13}=2c_{11}\zeta
. Then by (
A.1.34
) with
x=c_{13}(j-1)\delta t

\displaystyle\big(1+2c_{11}\Delta t\big)^{j-1}\leq\bigg(1+\frac{c_{13}(j-1)\delta t}{j-1}\bigg)^{j-1}\leq e^{c_{13}(j-1)\delta t},

where the right-hand side is bounded by
e^{c_{13}T}
independently of
j
. Consequently we derive from (
3.8.85
) and
\vartheta_{1}^{h\ell}=0
that

{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}\leq c_{12}e^{c_{13}(j-1)\delta t}\sum_{l=2}^{j}\delta t_{j}\,{\|\varrho_{l}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}\leq c_{14}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\delta t_{j}\,{\|\varrho_{l}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}.

(3.8.86)

where we have used that
\vartheta_{1}^{h\ell}=0
holds and
c_{14}=c_{12}e^{c_{13}T}
. By summation on
j=2,\ldots,n
we infer from
\vartheta_{1}^{h\ell}=0
,
\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}=T
, (
3.8.82
) and

\displaystyle\delta t_{j}\leq 2\alpha^{n}_{j},\quad\delta t_{j}\geq\frac{\delta t}{\Delta t}\,\Delta t\geq\frac{\Delta t}{\zeta}\geq\frac{\alpha^{n}_{j}}{\zeta}\quad\text{for }j=2,\ldots,n

that

\displaystyle{\|\vartheta_{n}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{\gamma_{1}}{\zeta}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha^{n}_{j}\,{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{\|\vartheta_{n}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+\gamma_{1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta t_{j}\,{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c_{11}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta t_{j}\,{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+c_{12}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta t_{j}\,{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2c_{11}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha^{n}_{j}\,{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+2c_{12}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha^{n}_{j}\,{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2c_{11}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha^{n}_{j}\,\bigg(c_{14}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\delta t_{l}\,{\|\varrho_{l}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}\bigg)+2c_{12}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha^{n}_{j}\,{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4c_{11}c_{14}T\,\bigg(\sum_{l=1}^{n}\alpha^{n}_{l}\,{\|\varrho_{l}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}\bigg)+2c_{12}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha^{n}_{j}\,{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\big(4c_{11}c_{14}T+2c_{12}\big)\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha^{n}_{j}\,{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}\leq c_{15}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha^{n}_{j}\,{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}

with
c_{15}=2(c_{11}c_{14}T+c_{12})
. Thus, we have

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha^{n}_{j}\,{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}\leq c_{16}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha^{n}_{j}\,{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}

(3.8.87)

with
c_{16}=c_{15}\zeta/\gamma_{1}
. Combining (
3.8.71
), (
3.8.87
) we get

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,{\|y_{j}^{h}-y_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}\leq 2\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{n}\,\left({\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}+{\|\vartheta_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}\right)\leq C\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha^{n}_{j}\,{\|\varrho_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}

with
C=2(1+c_{16})
. Now (
3.6.33
) follows from (
2.4.14a
) and (
2.4.14c
).
\Box

Proof of Theorem
3.6.14
.
We set
e^{h\ell}_{j}=y_{j}^{h}-y_{j}^{h\ell}\in V^{h}
for
j=1,\ldots,n
and
\overline{\partial}e_{j}^{h\ell}=(e^{h\ell}_{j}-e^{h\ell}_{j-1})/\delta t_{j}
for
j=2,\ldots,n
. Then we conclude that for every
\varphi\in V^{h}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\langle\overline{\partial}e^{h\ell}_{j},\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{H}+a(e^{h\ell}_{j},\varphi^{h})+{\langle\mathcal{C}e^{h\ell}_{j},\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

(3.8.88)

\displaystyle={\langle g_{j}(u)+\mathcal{N}(y_{j}^{h\ell})-\mathcal{N}(y_{j}^{h})-\overline{\partial}y_{j}^{h\ell}-\mathcal{C}y_{j}^{h\ell}-\mathcal{N}(y_{j}^{h\ell}),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}-a(y_{j}^{h\ell},\varphi^{h})

\displaystyle={\langle\mathcal{N}(y_{j}^{h\ell})-\mathcal{N}(y_{j}^{h}),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}+{\langle r_{j}^{h\ell},\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

with the residual

\displaystyle r_{j}^{h\ell}=-\left({\langle\overline{\partial}y_{j}^{h\ell}+\mathcal{C}y_{j}^{h\ell}+\mathcal{N}(y_{j}^{h\ell})-g_{j}(u),\cdot\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}+a(y_{j}^{h\ell},\cdot)\right)\in(V^{h})^{\prime}

for
j=2,\ldots,n
. We argue as for the a-posteriori error analysis carried out in Section
3.6.3
. Choosing
\varphi^{h}=e^{h\ell}_{j}\in V^{h}
in (
3.8.88
) we derive from (
3.6.3
) and Lemma
A.2.41
that

\displaystyle 2\,{\langle\overline{\partial}e_{j}^{h\ell},e_{j}^{h\ell}\rangle}_{H}+2a(e_{j}^{h\ell},e_{j}^{h\ell})+2\,{\langle\mathcal{C}e_{j}^{h\ell},e_{j}^{h\ell}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

(3.8.89)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\delta t_{j}}\left({\|e_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}-{\|e_{j-1}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+{\|e_{j}^{h\ell}-e_{j-1}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}\right)+2\gamma_{1}\,{\|e_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}

for
j=2,\ldots,n
. Since
\mathcal{M}
is bilinear, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(y^{h\ell}_{j})-\mathcal{N}(y^{h}_{j})=-\mathcal{M}(e^{h\ell}_{j},y^{h\ell}_{j})-\mathcal{M}(y^{h}_{j},e^{h\ell}_{j})

for
j=2,\ldots,n
(cf. (
3.8.65
)). From (
3.6.5
) we derive that

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{M}(y^{h}_{j},e^{h\ell}_{j}),e_{j}^{h\ell}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}=0

for
j=2,\ldots,n
and proceeding as in Section
3.6.3
we find

\displaystyle\big|{\langle\mathcal{M}(e^{h\ell}_{j},y^{h\ell}_{j}),e_{j}^{h\ell}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\big|\leq c_{\mathcal{M}}\,{\|e_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}{\|y_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}{\|e_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}

for
j=2,\ldots,n
. Thus, (
3.8.88
) and (
3.8.89
) imply that

\displaystyle{\|e_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}-{\|e_{j-1}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+{\|e_{j}^{h\ell}-e_{j-1}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+2\gamma_{1}\delta t_{j}\,{\|e_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2\delta t_{j}\left({\langle r^{h\ell}_{j},e_{j}^{h\ell}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}+{\langle\mathcal{N}(y_{j}^{h\ell})-\mathcal{N}(y_{j}^{h}),e_{j}^{h\ell}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2\delta t_{j}\,\left({\|r_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{(V^{h})^{\prime}}{\|e_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}+c_{\mathcal{M}}\,{\|e_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}{\|y_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}{\|e_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}\right)

for
j=2,\ldots,n
. Consequently, by using Young’s inequality we find that

\displaystyle{\|e_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}-{\|e_{j-1}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+\gamma_{1}\delta t_{j}\,{\|e_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}\leq\frac{\delta t_{j}}{\gamma_{1}}\,\bigg({\|r_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{(V^{h})^{\prime}}^{2}+c_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}\,{\|y_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}{\|e_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}\bigg).

for
j=2,\ldots,n
. By assumption there exists a constant
C>0
which is independent of
h
and
n
such that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq j\leq n}\,{\|y_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{V}^{2}\leq C^{2}.

We set
c_{2}=c_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}C^{2}/\gamma_{1}
. If
\Delta t\leq 1/(2c_{2})
holds, we derive

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-c_{2}\delta t_{j}}\leq 1+2c_{2}\Delta t.

Consequently, we get upon summation over
j

\displaystyle{\|e_{j}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}\leq\big(1+2c_{2}\Delta t\big)^{j-1}{\|e_{1}^{h\ell}\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\sum_{l=2}^{j}\big(1+2c_{2}\Delta t\big)^{j+1-l}\delta t_{l}\,{\|r_{l}^{h\ell}\|}_{(V^{h})^{\prime}}^{2}.

for
j\in\{2,\ldots,n\}
which gives the claim.
\Box

3.8.6
Proof of Section
3.7

Proof of Theorem
3.7.2
.

Since the bilinear form
a({\bm{\mu}};\cdot\,,\cdot)
is bounded and coercive on
V\times V
for every parameter
\mu\in\mathscr{D}
, the existence of a unique solution to (
3.7.2
) follows directly from the Lax-Milgram lemma;
see
[
Eva08
]
, for instance. Next we prove the a-priori estimate (
3.7.3
). For that purpose we take
\varphi=y\in V
in (
3.7.2
). It follows that

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}\,{\|y\|}_{V}^{2}\leq a({\bm{\mu}};y,y)={\langle\mathcal{F}({\bm{\mu}}),y\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\leq{\|\mathcal{F}({\bm{\mu}})\|}_{V^{\prime}}{\|y\|}_{V},

which gives the claim.
\Box

Chapter 4
Linear-Quadratic Optimal Control Problems

4.1
The abstract linear-quadratic control problem

In this section we introduce our abstract optimal control problem, which is a quadratic programming problem in a Hilbert space. Throughout this section we make use of the following assumption; cf. Assumption
3.2.1
.

Assumption 4.1.1
.

Let
T>0
hold.

1)

V
and
H
are real, separable Hilbert spaces and
V
is dense in
H
with compact embedding. We identify
H
with its dual (Hilbert) space
H^{\prime}
so that we have the Gelfand triple

\displaystyle V\hookrightarrow H\simeq H^{\prime}\hookrightarrow V^{\prime},

where each embedding is continuous and dense. Here, the embedding
H^{\prime}\hookrightarrow V^{\prime}
is given by
\langle\varphi^{\prime},\varphi\rangle_{V,V}=\langle\varphi^{\prime},\varphi\rangle_{H^{\prime},H}
for all
\varphi^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
,
\varphi\in V
.

2)

The
control space
{\mathscr{U}}
denotes either the Euclidean (Hilbert) space
\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}}
or the (Hilbert) space
L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}})
, where
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}_{\circ}}
is an open, bounded set with
\mathsf{m}_{\circ}\in\mathbb{N}
.

3)

For almost all
t\in[0,T]
, let
a(t;\cdot\,,\cdot):V\times V\to\mathbb{R}
be a
time-dependent bilinear form satisfying

\displaystyle\big|a(t;\varphi,\psi)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}{\|\psi\|}_{V}

\displaystyle\forall\varphi,\psi\in V\text{ a.e. in }[0,T],

(4.1.1a)

\displaystyle a(t;\varphi,\varphi)

\displaystyle\geq\gamma_{1}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}^{2}

\displaystyle\forall\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in }[0,T]

(4.1.1b)

for constants
\gamma\geq 0
and
\gamma_{1}>0
which do not depend on
t
.

4)

Assume that
y_{\circ}\in H
,
\mathcal{F}\in L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})
and that
\mathcal{B}:{\mathscr{U}}\to L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})
is a continuous, linear and injective
control operator.

Remark 4.1.2
.

In
{\mathscr{U}}=\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}}
we understand the inequality
u\leq\tilde{u}
componentwise for
u=(u_{j})_{1\leq j\leq\mathsf{m}}
,
\tilde{u}=(\tilde{u}_{j})_{1\leq j\leq\mathsf{m}}\in{\mathscr{U}}
, i.e., we have
u_{j}\leq\tilde{u}_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,\mathsf{m}
. If
{\mathscr{U}}=L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}})
holds, we interpret
u\leq\tilde{u}
for
u=(u_{j})_{1\leq j\leq\mathsf{m}}
,
\tilde{u}=(\tilde{u}_{j})_{1\leq j\leq\mathsf{m}}\in{\mathscr{U}}
as
u(s)\leq\tilde{u}(s)
f.a.a.
s\in\mathscr{D}
.
\blacksquare

4.1.1
Problem formulation

First we formulate our state equation which coincides with the linear evolution problem introduced in (
3.2.4
). For
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
we consider the state equation

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle y(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;y(t),\varphi)

\displaystyle={\langle(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}~\forall\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

(4.1.2)

\displaystyle{\langle y(0),\varphi\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle={\langle y_{\circ},\varphi\rangle}_{H}\hskip 56.9055pt\forall\varphi\in H.

Remark 4.1.3
.

To simplify the presentation we have replaced (
3.2.1b
) by the stronger assumption (
4.1.1b
) in Assumption
4.1.1
. However, in some applications (
4.1.1b
) can not be guaranteed, but the bilinear form
a(t;\cdot\,,\cdot)
satisfies weaker estimate (
3.2.1b
); cf. Section
3.1
. Then, we define the time-dependent bilinear form

\displaystyle\tilde{a}(t;\varphi,\psi)=a(t;\varphi,\psi)+\gamma_{2}\,{\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle}_{H}\quad\text{for }\varphi,\psi\in V\text{ a.e. in }[0,T].

Clearly,
\tilde{a}
satisfies (
3.2.1b
). By (
2.4.1
) we derive that

\displaystyle\big|\tilde{a}(t;\varphi,\psi)\big|\leq\gamma\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}{\|\psi\|}_{V}+\gamma_{2}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{H}{\|\psi\|}_{H}\leq\tilde{\gamma}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}{\|\psi\|}_{V}\quad\text{for all }\varphi,\psi\in V

with
\tilde{\gamma}=\gamma+\gamma_{2}c_{V}^{2}>0
. Thus, the bilinear form
\tilde{a}(t;\cdot\,,\cdot)
is also bounded. Let

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(t)=e^{-\gamma_{2}t}\mathcal{F}(t),\quad(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}u)(t)=e^{-\gamma_{2}t}(\mathcal{B}u)(t)\quad\text{f.a.a. }t\in[0,T].

Then it clearly holds
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}\in L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})
,
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathscr{L}({\mathscr{U}};L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime}))
. Instead of (
4.1.2
) we consider the evolution problem

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle\tilde{y}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\tilde{y}(t),\varphi)

\displaystyle={\langle(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}+\tilde{\mathcal{B}}u)(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

\displaystyle\text{for all }\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

(4.1.3)

\displaystyle{\langle\tilde{y}(0),\varphi\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle={\langle y_{\circ},\varphi\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle\text{for all }\varphi\in H.

for any
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
and
y_{\circ}\in H
. Due to Theorem
3.2.4
there exists a unique solution
\tilde{y}\in{\mathscr{Y}}
to (
4.1.3
). Then we define

\displaystyle y(t)=e^{\gamma_{2}t}\tilde{y}(t)\quad\text{f.a.a. }t\in[0,T].

Now we find for all
\varphi\in V

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle y(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;y(t),\varphi)=\gamma_{2}\,{\langle y(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+e^{\gamma_{2}t}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle\tilde{y}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;y(t),\varphi)

\displaystyle\quad=\tilde{a}(t;y(t),\varphi)+e^{\gamma_{2}t}\Big({\langle(\tilde{f}+\tilde{\mathcal{B}}u)(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}-\tilde{a}(t;\tilde{y}(t),\varphi)\big)={\langle(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}.

As
y(0)=\tilde{y}(0)
holds,
y
solves (
3.2.4
).
\blacksquare

Since Assumption
4.1.1
holds, Theorem
3.2.4
ensures the existence of a unique solution
y
belonging to the
state space

{\mathscr{Y}}=W(0,T)
to (
4.1.2
). In Corollary
3.2.5
we have introduced the particular solution
\hat{y}\in{\mathscr{Y}}
as well as the linear, bounded solution operator
\mathcal{S}
. Then the solution to (
4.1.2
) can be expressed as
y=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u
.

Remark 4.1.4
.

Since the operator
\mathcal{B}
is injective, we can conclude that the operator
\mathcal{S}
is injective as well: Let
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
be given with
\mathcal{S}u=0
, then it immediately follows from (
3.2.7
) that
\langle(\mathcal{B}u)(t),\varphi\rangle_{V^{\prime}\times V}=0
for all
\varphi\in V
. This is equivalent to
\mathcal{B}u=0
in
L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})
, which yields
u=0
due to the fact that
\mathcal{B}
is injective. Therefore,
\mathcal{S}
is injective.
\blacksquare

Definition 4.1.5
.

We define the Hilbert space

\displaystyle\mathscr{X}={\mathscr{Y}}\times{\mathscr{U}}

endowed with the natural product topology, i.e., with the inner product

\displaystyle{\langle x,\tilde{x}\rangle}_{\mathscr{X}}={\langle y,\tilde{y}\rangle}_{\mathscr{Y}}+{\langle u,\tilde{u}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}\quad\text{for }x=(y,u),~\tilde{x}=(\tilde{y},\tilde{u})\in{\mathscr{X}}

and the norm
\|x\|_{\mathscr{X}}=(\|y\|_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}+\|u\|_{\mathscr{U}}^{2})^{1/2}
for
x=(y,u)\in{\mathscr{X}}
.

In a next step we introduce the quadratic objective function. For given desired states
({y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}},{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}})\in L^{2}(0,T;H)\times H
, a nominal control
{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\in{\mathscr{U}}
and non-negative weighting parameters
\sigma_{1}
,
\sigma_{2}
and
\sigma
the
quadratic cost functional

J:{\mathscr{X}}\to\mathbb{R}
is given by

J(x)=\frac{\sigma_{1}}{2}\int_{0}^{T}{\|y(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\sigma_{2}}{2}\,{\|y(T)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{\sigma}{2}\,{\|u-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}

(4.1.4)

for
x=(y,u)\in{\mathscr{X}}
. Then given the control bounds
u_{\mathsf{a}},\,u_{\mathsf{b}}\in{\mathscr{U}}
with
u_{a}\leq u_{b}
we consider the quadratic programming problem

\min J(x)\quad\mbox{subject to (s.t.)}\quad x=(y,u)\in{\mathscr{X}},~y=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u\text{ and }u_{\mathsf{a}}\leq u\leq u_{\mathsf{b}}.

(
P)

Definition 4.1.6
.

The set of admissible controls is given as

\displaystyle{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}=\big\{u\in{\mathscr{U}}\,\big|\,u_{\mathsf{a}}\leq u\leq u_{\mathsf{b}}\text{ in }{\mathscr{U}}\big\}.

By
{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}\subset{\mathscr{X}}
we denote the admissible set for the optimization problem as

\displaystyle{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}=\big\{(y,u)\in{\mathscr{X}}\,\big|\,y=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u\text{ and }u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}\big\}.

With this definition we can write the
quadratic programming problem
in the form

\min J(x)\quad\mbox{subject to (s.t.)}\quad x\in{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

(
P)

Remark 4.1.7
.

From
x=(y,u)\in{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
we infer that
y=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u
holds. Hence,
y
is a dependent variable and (
P
) is called a
linear-quadratic optimal control problem
. Moreover, we call
y
the
state variable
and
u
the
control variable
.
\blacksquare

We summarize the framework of the optimal control problem in the following assumption.

Assumption 4.1.8
.

1)

The pair
({y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}},{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}})
of desired states belongs to
L^{2}(0,T;H)\times H
and
{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\in{\mathscr{U}}
is a nominal control. Moreover,
\sigma_{1}
,
\sigma_{2}
and
\sigma
are non-negative weighting parameters.

2)

For
u_{\mathsf{a}},u_{\mathsf{b}}\in{\mathscr{U}}
it holds
u_{\mathsf{a}}\leq u_{\mathsf{b}}
.

3)

If
{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is unbounded (i.e., at least one of the components of
u_{\mathsf{a}}
or
u_{\mathsf{b}}
is
-\infty
or
\infty
, respectively) we have
\sigma>0
.

Remark 4.1.9
.

1)

The set
{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is closed and convex. Since
\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{L}({\mathscr{U}},{\mathscr{Y}})
holds,
{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is closed and convex as well.

2)

From Assumption
4.1.8
-2) and Theorem
3.2.4
we conclude that the set
{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is nonempty.

3)

More general quadratic cost functionals can be treated as well. For instance, the presentation can be extended to the following quadratic cost functional:

\displaystyle J(x)=\frac{\sigma_{1}}{2}\,{\|\mathcal{C}_{1}y-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}\|}_{{\mathscr{W}}_{1}}^{2}+\frac{\sigma_{2}}{2}\,{\|\mathcal{C}_{2}(y(T))-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{{\mathscr{W}}_{2}}^{2}+\frac{\sigma}{2}\,{\|u-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}

for
x=(y,u)\in{\mathscr{X}}
, where
{\mathscr{W}}_{1}
,
{\mathscr{W}}_{2}
are Hilbert spaces,
\mathcal{C}_{1}:{\mathscr{Y}}\to{\mathscr{W}}_{1}
,
\mathcal{C}_{2}:H\to{\mathscr{W}}_{2}
are bounded, linear
observation operators
,
({y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}},{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}})\in{\mathscr{W}}_{1}\times{\mathscr{W}}_{2}
,
{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\in{\mathscr{U}}
are given data functions,
\sigma_{1}
,
\sigma_{2}\geq 0
and
\sigma>0
.
\blacksquare

Definition 4.1.10
.

A point
\bar{x}=(\bar{y},\bar{u})\in{\mathscr{X}}
is called a globally optimal solution to (
P
) if
\bar{x}\in{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
and
J(\bar{x})\leq J(x)
for all
x\in{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
hold. We say that
\bar{x}=(\bar{y},\bar{u})\in{\mathscr{X}}
is a locally optimal solution to (
P
) if there exists an open set
\mathscr{N}(\bar{x})\subset{\mathscr{X}}
containing
\bar{x}
such that
\bar{x}\in{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
and
J(\bar{x})\leq J(x)
for all
x\in{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}\cap\mathscr{N}(\bar{x})
are satisfied.

The proof of the next lemma is given in Section
4.5.1
.

Lemma 4.1.11
.

1)

Let Assumption
4.1.8
-1)
hold. Then
J
is convex.

2)

Suppose that Assumptions
4.1.1
and
4.1.8
are satisfied. Then every locally optimal solution to (
P
) is a globally optimal one.

Utilizing the relationship
y=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u
we define a so-called
reduced cost functional

\hat{J}:{\mathscr{U}}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle\hat{J}(u)=J(\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u,u)\quad\text{for }u\in{\mathscr{U}}.

Then we consider the
reduced optimal control problem
:

\min\hat{J}(u)\quad\text{s.t.}\quad u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

(
\mathbf{\hat{P}}
)

The proof of the next lemma is straightforward. Therefore, we omit the proof here.

Lemma 4.1.12
.

Let Assumptions
4.1.1
and
4.1.8
hold. Then if
\bar{u}
is the optimal solution to (

^
P

), then
\bar{x}=(\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}\bar{u},\bar{u})
is the optimal solution to (
P
). On the other hand, if
\bar{x}=(\bar{y},\bar{u})
is the solution to (
P
), then
\bar{u}
solves (

^
P

).

Example 4.1.13
.

An example for (
P
) is the one given in Sections
1.2
and
3.1
. We discuss the presented theory for this application. Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{n}}
,
\mathfrak{n}\in\{1,2,3\}
, be an open and bounded domain with Lipschitz-continuous boundary
\Gamma=\partial\Omega
. For
T>0
we set
Q=(0,T)\times\Omega
and
\Sigma=(0,T)\times\Gamma
. We choose
H=L^{2}(\Omega)
and
V=H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
endowed with the usual inner products

\displaystyle{\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle}_{H}=\int_{\Omega}\varphi\psi\,\mathrm{d}\bm{x},\quad{\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle}_{V}=\int_{\Omega}\varphi\psi+\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\psi\,\mathrm{d}\bm{x}

and their induced norms, respectively. We choose
\mathscr{D}=(0,T)
and
{\mathscr{U}}=L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}})
. Let
\chi_{i}\in H
,
1\leq i\leq\mathsf{m}
, denote given control shape functions. Then for given control
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
, initial condition
y_{\circ}\in H
, diffusion coefficient
c({\bm{x}})\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
with
c({\bm{x}})\geq c_{0}>0
for all
{\bm{x}}\in\Omega
, reaction coefficient
d({\bm{x}})\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
with
d({\bm{x}})\geq d_{0}>0
for all
{\bm{x}}\in\Omega
, non-negative
q\in L^{\infty}(\Gamma)
, inhomogeneity
f\in L^{2}(0,T;H)
and boundary inhomogeneity
g\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Gamma))
we consider the linear parabolic
diffusion-reaction equation
(
1.2.1
)

\displaystyle y_{t}(t,{\bm{x}})-\kappa\Delta y(t,{\bm{x}}))+{\bm{v}}({\bm{x}})\cdot\nabla y(t,{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=f(t,{\bm{x}})+\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}u^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}_{i}(t)\chi_{i}({\bm{x}}),

\displaystyle(t,{\bm{x}})\in Q,

\displaystyle\kappa\frac{\partial y}{\partial{\bm{n}}}(t,{\bm{s}})+q({\bm{s}})y(t,{\bm{s}})

\displaystyle=g(t,{\bm{s}})+\sum_{j=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}}u^{\mathsf{b}}_{j}(t)\xi_{j}({\bm{s}}),

\displaystyle(t,{\bm{s}})\in\Sigma,

\displaystyle y(0,{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=y_{\circ}(0),

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\bm{x}}\in\Omega.

We introduce the time-independent, symmetric bilinear form

\displaystyle a(\varphi,\psi)=\kappa\int_{\Omega}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\psi+\big({\bm{v}}\cdot\nabla\varphi\big)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\bm{x}+\int_{\Gamma}q\varphi\psi\,\mathrm{d}s\quad\text{ for }\varphi,\psi\in V

the bounded time-dependent linear operator
\mathcal{F}(t):V\to V^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{F}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}=\int_{\Omega}f(t)\varphi\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}+\int_{\Gamma}g(t)\varphi\mathrm{d}s\quad\text{for }\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in }(0,T)

and the bounded, linear and injective operator
\mathcal{B}:{\mathscr{U}}\to L^{2}(0,T;H)\hookrightarrow L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{B}u)(t,\bm{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}u^{\mathsf{d}}_{i}(t)\chi_{i}(\bm{x})\,+\,\sum_{j=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}}u^{\mathsf{b}}_{j}(t)\xi_{j}({\bm{x}})\quad\text{for }(t,\bm{x})\in Q\text{ a.e. and }u\in{\mathscr{U}}.

Applying Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and Theorem
3.1.3
, one can derive the following estimate:

\displaystyle\big|a(\varphi,\psi)\big|

\displaystyle\leq{\|c\|}_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}{\|\varphi\|}_{V}{\|\psi\|}_{V}+{\|d\|}_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}{\|\varphi\|}_{H}{\|\psi\|}_{H}+\gamma_{\Gamma}^{2}{\|q\|}_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}{\|\varphi\|}_{V}{\|\psi\|}_{V}\leq\gamma\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}{\|\psi\|}_{V}

with
\gamma=\|c\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+\|d\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+\gamma_{\Gamma}^{2}\|q\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle a(\varphi,\varphi)\geq\gamma_{1}\|\varphi\|_{V}^{2}

with
\gamma_{1}=\min(c_{0},d_{0})
. Hence,
a(\cdot,\cdot)
satisfies (
4.1.1a
) and (
4.1.1b
). From Remark
4.1.3
it follows that the weak formulation of (
1.2.1
) can be expressed in the form (
3.2.4
). Moreover, the unique weak solution to (
1.2.1
) belongs to the space
L^{\infty}(0,T;V)
provided
y_{\circ}\in V
holds.
\blacklozenge

4.1.2
Existence of a unique optimal solution

Next, we will review an existence result for quadratic optimization problems in Hilbert spaces. While we have previously denoted the cost function in this chapter with
\hat{J}
, the following result from
[
Trö10
, pp. 50-51]
will use a more general cost function
\mathcal{J}
.

Theorem 4.1.14
.

Suppose that
{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}
and
{\mathscr{H}}
are given Hilbert spaces with norms
\|\cdot\|_{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{H}}
, respectively. Furthermore, let
{{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}\subset{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}
be non-empty, closed, convex and
{y^{\mathsf{d}}}\in{\mathscr{H}}
,
\tilde{u}^{\mathsf{n}}\in{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}
,
\sigma\geq 0
. Moreover, let either
{{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
be bounded or
\sigma>0
hold. The mapping
\mathcal{G}\,:{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}\to{\mathscr{H}}
is assumed to be linear and continuous. Then there exists an optimal control
\bar{u}
solving

\min\mathcal{J}(u):=\frac{1}{2}\,{\|\mathcal{G}u-{y^{\mathsf{d}}}\|}_{\mathscr{H}}^{2}+\frac{\sigma}{2}\,{\|u-\tilde{u}^{\mathsf{n}}\|}_{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}^{2}\quad\text{s.t.}\quad u\in{{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

(4.1.5)

If
\sigma>0
holds or if
\mathcal{G}
is injective, then
\bar{u}
is uniquely determined.

Remark 4.1.15
.

In the proof of Theorem
4.1.14
it is only used that
\mathcal{J}
is continuous and convex and that, in the case of
{{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
being unbounded, it holds
\lim_{{\|u\|}_{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}\to\infty}\mathcal{J}(u)=\infty
. Therefore, the existence of an optimal control follows for general cost functionals
\mathcal{J}:{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}\to\mathbb{R}
with a Hilbert space
{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}
fulfilling these properties.
\blacksquare

Next we can use Theorem
4.1.14
to obtain an existence result for the optimal control problem (

^
P

), which implies the existence of an optimal solution to (
P
). For a proof we refer the reader to Section
4.5.1
.

Theorem 4.1.16
.

Let Assumptions
4.1.1
and
4.1.8
be satisfied. Then (

^
P

) possesses a solution
\bar{u}
. If
\sigma_{1}>0
or
\sigma>0
the solution is unique.

4.1.3
First-order sufficient optimality conditions

Existence of a unique solution to (
4.1.5
) has been investigated in Section
4.1.2
. In this section we characterize the solution to (
4.1.5
) by first-order sufficient optimality conditions. To derive first-order conditions we require the notion of Gâteaux- and Fréchet-derivatives in function spaces; see Definition
A.4.61
.

Remark 4.1.17
.

Let
{\mathscr{H}}
be a real Hilbert space and
F:{\mathscr{H}}\to\mathbb{R}
be Gâteaux-differentiable at
u\in\mathscr{H}
. Then its Gâteaux derivative
F^{\prime}(u)
at
u
belongs to
{\mathscr{H}}^{\prime}=\mathscr{L}({\mathscr{H}},\mathbb{R})
. Due to the Riesz theorem there exists a unique element
\nabla F(u)\in\mathscr{H}
satisfying

\displaystyle{\langle\nabla F(u),v\rangle}_{\mathscr{H}}={\langle F^{\prime}(u),v\rangle}_{{\mathscr{H}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{H}}}\quad\text{for all }v\in{\mathscr{H}}.

We call
\nabla F(u)
the
(Gâteaux) gradient
of
F
at
u
.
\blacksquare

For a proof of the next lemma we refer to Section
4.5.1
.

Lemma 4.1.18
.

Let all hypotheses of Theorem
4.1.14
hold. Then
\mathcal{J}
defined in (
4.1.5
) is Gateaux-differentiable in
{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}
. Its Gateaux derivative is given as

\mathcal{J}^{\prime}(u)={\langle\mathcal{G}u-{y^{\mathsf{d}}},\mathcal{G}(\cdot)\rangle}_{\mathscr{H}}+\sigma\,{\langle u-\tilde{u}^{\mathsf{n}},\cdot\rangle}_{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}\quad\text{for }u\in{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}.

(4.1.6)

Remark 4.1.19
.

The gradient
\nabla\mathcal{J}
of
\mathcal{J}
introduced in Remark
4.1.17
is given as

\nabla\mathcal{J}(u)=\mathcal{G}^{\star}(\mathcal{G}u-{y^{\mathsf{d}}})+\sigma\,(u-\tilde{u}^{\mathsf{n}})\quad\text{for }u\in{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}

(4.1.7)

with the adjoint operator
\mathcal{G}^{\star}\in\mathscr{L}({\mathscr{H}},{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}})
.
\blacksquare

In the next theorem, which is proved in Section
4.5.1
, we formulate the first-order necessary optimality conditions for (
4.1.5
). An essential assumption is that the set
{{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
of admissible controls is non-empty and convex.

Theorem 4.1.20

(First-order necessary optimality conditions)
.

Let all assumptions of Theorem
4.1.14
hold. Suppose that
\bar{u}\in{{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is a solution to (
4.1.5
). Then the following variational inequality holds

{\langle\nabla\mathcal{J}(\bar{u}),u-\bar{u}\rangle}_{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}\geq 0\quad\text{for all }u\in{{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}_{\mathsf{ad}}},

(4.1.8)

where the gradient of
\mathcal{J}
is given by (
4.1.7
). If
\bar{u}\in{{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
solves (
4.1.8
), then
\bar{u}
is a solution to (
4.1.5
).

Remark 4.1.21
.

Combining (
4.1.7
) and (
4.1.8
) we find that the variational inequality

{\langle\mathcal{G}\bar{u}-{y^{\mathsf{d}}},\mathcal{G}(u-\bar{u})\rangle}_{\mathscr{H}}+{\langle\sigma\,(\bar{u}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}),u-\bar{u}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}\geq 0\quad\text{for all }u\in{{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}

(4.1.9)

is a first-order sufficient optimality condition for (
4.1.5
).
\blacksquare

From now on we assume the situation given in the proof of Theorem
4.1.16
, i.e. the operator
\mathcal{G}
has the form like in (
4.5.2
) and the cost function is specifically given by
\hat{J}
instead of the general
\mathcal{J}
from (
4.1.16
). Our aim is to find a representation of the gradient
\nabla\mathcal{J}
in (
4.1.7
) which can be evaluated numerically. To do this, we need to find an equivalent, more practical representation of the adjoint operator
\mathcal{G}^{\star}
. This task will be completed in Lemma
4.1.24
, but first we require some preparations in the form of the the so-called adjoint equation. A proof of the next lemma is given in Section
4.5.1
.

Lemma 4.1.22
.

Suppose that Assumptions
4.1.1
and
4.1.8
hold. Let us define the linear operator
\mathcal{A}:{\mathscr{U}}\to{\mathscr{Y}}
as follows: For given
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
the function
p=\mathcal{A}u\in{\mathscr{Y}}
is the unique solution to

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle p(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\varphi,p(t))

\displaystyle=-\sigma_{1}\,{\langle(\mathcal{S}u)(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle\text{for all }\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in }[0,T),

(4.1.10)

\displaystyle p(T)

\displaystyle=-\sigma_{2}\,(\mathcal{S}u)(T)

\displaystyle\text{in }H.

1)

The operator
\mathcal{A}
is well-defined and bounded.

2)

We set
y=\mathcal{S}u\in W_{0}(0,T)
,
w=\mathcal{S}v\in W_{0}(0,T)
, and
p=\mathcal{A}v\in{\mathscr{Y}}
. Then

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}{\langle(\mathcal{B}u)(t),p(t)\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\,\mathrm{d}t=-\int_{0}^{T}\sigma_{1}\,{\langle w(t),y(t)\rangle}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}t-\sigma_{2}\,{\langle w(T),y(T)\rangle}_{H}.

Remark 4.1.23
.

We define
\hat{p}\in{\mathscr{Y}}
as the unique solution to

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle\hat{p}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\varphi,\hat{p}(t))

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\,{\langle{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)-\hat{y}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle\text{for all }\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in }[0,T),

(4.1.11)

\displaystyle\hat{p}(T)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\,({y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-\hat{y}(T))

\displaystyle\text{in }H.

Then for every
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
the function
p=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}u
is the unique solution to

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle p(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\varphi,p(t))

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\,{\langle{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)-y(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle\text{for all }\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in }[0,T),

(4.1.12a)

\displaystyle p(T)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\,({y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-y(T))

\displaystyle\text{in }H

(4.1.12b)

with
y=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u
.
\blacksquare

Moreover, we have the following result which yields a computable formula for the gradient of the reduced cost functional. For the proof we refer the reader to Section
4.5.1
.

Lemma 4.1.24
.

Let Assumptions
4.1.1
and
4.1.8
hold. Let us define the two linear, bounded operators
\Theta:W_{0}(0,T)\to W_{0}(0,T)^{\prime}
and
\Xi:L^{2}(0,T;H)\times H\to W_{0}(0,T)^{\prime}
by

\displaystyle{\langle\Theta\varphi,\phi\rangle}_{W_{0}(0,T)^{\prime},W_{0}(0,T)}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\sigma_{1}\,{\langle\varphi(t),\phi(t)\rangle}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{2}\,{\langle\varphi(T),\phi(T)\rangle}_{H},

(4.1.13)

\displaystyle{\langle\Xi z,\phi\rangle}_{W_{0}(0,T)^{\prime},W_{0}(0,T)}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\sigma_{1}\,{\langle z_{1}(t),\phi(t)\rangle}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{2}\,{\langle z_{2},\phi(T)\rangle}_{H}

for
\varphi,\,\phi\in W_{0}(0,T)
and
z=(z_{1},z_{2})\in L^{2}(0,T;H)\times H
.

1)

We have

\mathcal{G}^{\star}(\mathcal{G}u-{y^{\mathsf{d}}})=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\Theta\mathcal{S}u-\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\Xi(y_{1}^{\mathsf{d}}-\hat{y},y_{2}^{\mathsf{d}}-\hat{y}(T))

(4.1.14)

for all
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
, where
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}:{\mathscr{Y}}^{\prime}\to{\mathscr{U}}^{\prime}\simeq{\mathscr{U}}
is the dual operator of
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\star}\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H},\tilde{\mathscr{U}})
is the Hilbert adjoint of
\mathcal{G}
defined in (
4.5.2
).

2)

Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\mathcal{A}=-\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\Theta\mathcal{S}\text{ in }\mathscr{L}({\mathscr{U}})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\hat{p}=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\Xi({y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}-\hat{y},{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-\hat{y}(T)),

(4.1.15)

where the linear and bounded operator
\mathcal{A}
has been defined in Lemma
4.1.22
.

Remark 4.1.25
.

Combining the previous lemmata, we can now express the gradient of the function
\hat{J}
in (
4.1.5
) in a more explicit way. In fact, applying (
4.1.7
), (
4.1.14
) and (
4.1.15
) we get

\displaystyle\nabla\hat{J}(u)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{G}^{\star}(\mathcal{G}u-{y^{\mathsf{d}}})+\sigma(u-\tilde{u}^{\mathsf{n}})

(4.1.16)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\Theta\mathcal{S}u-\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\Xi(y_{1}^{\mathsf{d}}-\hat{y},y_{2}^{\mathsf{d}}-\hat{y}(T))+\sigma(u-\tilde{u}^{\mathsf{n}})

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\mathcal{A}u-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\hat{p}+\sigma(u-\tilde{u}^{\mathsf{n}})=-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}p+\sigma(u-\tilde{u}^{\mathsf{n}}),

where we have defined the adjoint variable
p=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}u
that solves the adjoint equation (
4.1.12
).
\blacksquare

The following result is just Theorem
4.1.20
, where we have explicitly used the representation of the gradient
\nabla\hat{J}
from Remark
4.1.25
for the optimal variable
u=\bar{u}
.

Theorem 4.1.26
.

Suppose that Assumptions
4.1.1
and
4.1.8
hold. Then
\bar{x}=(\bar{y},\bar{u})
is a solution to (
P
) if and only if
\bar{x}
together with the adjoint variable
\bar{p}
satisfy the first-order optimality system

\displaystyle\bar{y}=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}\bar{u},\qquad\bar{p}=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}\bar{u},\qquad\bar{u}\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}},

(4.1.17a)

\displaystyle{\langle\sigma(\bar{u}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p},u-\bar{u}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}\geq 0\quad\forall u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

(4.1.17b)

It turns out that (
4.1.17
) is only one of several equivalent optimality conditions.
If we apply
[
HPUU09
, Lemma 1.12]
to our problem, we achieve the following result.

Proposition 4.1.27
.

Let Assumptions
4.1.1
and
4.1.8
hold. Due to (
4.1.16
) the gradient
\nabla\hat{J}
of the reduced cost functional
\hat{J}
at
\bar{u}
is given by
\nabla\hat{J}(\bar{u})=\sigma(\bar{u}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p}\in{\mathscr{U}}
with
\bar{p}=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}\bar{u}
. Then the following four statements are equivalent:

1)

\bar{u}\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
and

\displaystyle{\langle\nabla\hat{J}(\bar{u}),u-\bar{u}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}\geq 0\quad\text{for all }u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}

2)

\bar{u}\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
and in case of
{\mathscr{U}}=\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}}

\displaystyle(\nabla\hat{J}(\bar{u}))_{i}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &=0&&\text{if }u_{\mathsf{a}i}<\bar{u}_{i}<u_{\mathsf{b}i},\\
&\geq 0&&\text{if }u_{\mathsf{a}i}=\bar{u}_{i}<u_{\mathsf{b}i},\\
&\leq 0&&\text{if }u_{\mathsf{a}i}<\bar{u}_{i}=u_{\mathsf{b}i},\end{aligned}\right.\quad 1\leq i\leq\mathsf{m},

in case of
{\mathscr{U}}=L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}})

\displaystyle(\nabla\hat{J}(\bar{u}))_{i}(s)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &=0&&\text{if }u_{\mathsf{a}i}(s)<\bar{u}_{i}(s)<u_{\mathsf{b}i}(s),\\
&\geq 0&&\text{if }u_{\mathsf{a}i}(s)=\bar{u}_{i}(s)<u_{\mathsf{b}i}(s),\\
&\leq 0&&\text{if }u_{\mathsf{a}i}(s)<\bar{u}_{i}(s)=u_{\mathsf{b}i}(s),\end{aligned}\right.\quad 1\leq i\leq\mathsf{m}\text{ a.e. in }\mathscr{D},

respectively.

3)

The two functions

\displaystyle\bar{\mu}_{\mathsf{a}}=\max\big\{\nabla\hat{J}(\bar{u}),0\big\}\text{ in }{\mathscr{U}}\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{\mu}_{\mathsf{b}}=-\min\big\{\nabla\hat{J}(\bar{u}),0\big\}\text{ in }{\mathscr{U}}

satisfy

\displaystyle\bar{u}\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}},\quad\bar{\mu}_{a},\bar{\mu}_{b}\geq 0\text{ in }{\mathscr{U}},\quad{\langle\bar{\mu}_{\mathsf{a}},\bar{u}-u_{\mathsf{a}}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}={\langle\bar{\mu}_{\mathsf{b}},u_{\mathsf{b}}-\bar{u}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}=0.

We call
\bar{\mu}_{\mathsf{a}}
and
\bar{\mu}_{\mathsf{b}}
Lagrange multipliers associated with the inequality constraints
\bar{u}-u_{\mathsf{a}}\leq 0
in
{\mathscr{U}}
and
\bar{u}-u_{\mathsf{b}}\leq 0
in
{\mathscr{U}}
, respectively.

4)

For any
\eta>0
we have

\displaystyle\bar{u}=\mathcal{P}_{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}(\bar{u}-\eta\nabla\hat{J}(\bar{u})).

4.1.4
A Lagrangian-based approach for (
P
)

Until now, we have dealt with the optimal control problem (
P
) by using the solution operator
\mathcal{S}
. This approach allowed us to introduce the reduced cost function
\hat{J}
which led to the equivalent problem (

^
P

) in which the state equation
y=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u
did not explicitly occur. In this section, we will present an equivalent, Lagrangian-based approach by writing the state equation as a specific equality constraint
e(y,u)=0
. To do so, let us define the product Hilbert space

\displaystyle{\mathscr{P}}=L^{2}(0,T;V)\times H

endowed with the canonical inner product. We identify
{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime}
with
L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})\times H
and introduce the affine-linear operator
e=(e_{1},e_{2}):{\mathscr{X}}\to{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle{\langle e_{1}(x),p^{1}\rangle}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime}),L^{2}(0,T;V)}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}{\langle y_{t}(t),p^{1}(t)\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}+a(t;y(t),p^{1}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t

(4.1.18)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}{\langle(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(t),p^{1}(t)\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\,\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle{\langle e_{2}(x),p^{2}\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle=\langle y(0)-y_{\circ},p^{2}\rangle_{H}

for
x=(y,u)\in{\mathscr{X}}
and
p=(p^{1},p^{2})\in{\mathscr{P}}
. Then (
4.1.2
) can be expressed equivalently by the equality constraint

\displaystyle e(x)=0\quad\text{in }{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime}.

In particular, we infer that

e(\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u,u)=0\quad\text{for all }u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

(4.1.19)

Now (
P
) can be written as a quadratic programming problem with equality and inequality constraints:

\min J(x)\quad\text{s.t.}\quad x=(y,u)\in{\mathscr{X}},~e(x)=0\text{ in }{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime}\text{ and }u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

(
QP)

Let
\bar{u}\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
be the unique solution to (

^
P

). Then
\bar{x}=(\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}\bar{u},\bar{u})\in{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is the unique solution to (
QP
). The Lagrange function for (
QP
) is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(x,p)=J(x)+{\langle e(x),p\rangle}_{{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{P}}}=J(x)+{\langle e_{1}(x),p^{1}\rangle}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime}),L^{2}(0,T;V)}+{\langle e_{2}(x),p^{2}\rangle}_{H}

for
x=(y,u)\in{\mathscr{X}}
and
p=(p^{1},p^{2})\in{\mathscr{P}}
. It follows from (
4.1.19
) that

\hat{J}(u)=J(\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u,u)=\mathcal{L}(\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u,u,p)

(4.1.20)

for every
u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
and
p\in{\mathscr{P}}
.

To characterize the solution to (
QP
) we derive first-order optimality conditions. Therefore, we will show that the Lagrangian is Fréchet-differentiable; cf. Definition
A.4.61
. For a proof we refer to Section
4.5.1
.

Lemma 4.1.28
.

Let Assumption
4.1.8
be valid. The mapping
J
is continuously Fréchet-differentiable from
{\mathscr{X}}
to
\mathbb{R}
and

J^{\prime}(x)x^{\delta}=\sigma_{1}\int_{0}^{T}{\langle y(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t),y^{\delta}(t)\rangle}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{2}\,{\langle y(T)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}},y^{\delta}(T)\rangle}_{H}+\sigma\,{\langle u-{u^{\mathsf{n}}},u^{\delta}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}

(4.1.21)

for
x=(y,u)\in{\mathscr{X}}
and
x^{\delta}=(y^{\delta},u^{\delta})\in{\mathscr{X}}
.

Remark 4.1.29
.

Note that
J^{\prime}(x)
is an element in
{\mathscr{X}}^{\prime}
for any
x\in{\mathscr{X}}
. Thus, we can also write
J^{\prime}(x)x^{\delta}=\langle J^{\prime}(x),x^{\delta}\rangle_{{\mathscr{X}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{X}}}
for every
x^{\delta}\in{\mathscr{X}}
.
\blacksquare

In Section
4.5.1
the following result is proved.

Lemma 4.1.30
.

Let Assumption
4.1.1
hold. Then the operator
e:{\mathscr{X}}\to\Lambda^{\prime}
is continuously Fréchet-differentiable in
{\mathscr{X}}
. In particular, we have

\displaystyle{\langle e_{1}^{\prime}(x)x^{\delta},p^{1}\rangle}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime}),L^{2}(0,T;V)}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}{\langle y^{\delta}_{t}(t),p^{1}(t)\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}+a(t;y^{\delta}(t),p^{1}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t

(4.1.22)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}{\langle(\mathcal{B}u^{\delta})(t),p^{1}(t)\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\,\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle e_{2}^{\prime}(x)x^{\delta}

\displaystyle=y^{\delta}(0)

for
x=(y,u)\in{\mathscr{X}}
,
x^{\delta}=(y^{\delta},u^{\delta})\in{\mathscr{X}}
and
p^{1}\in L^{2}(0,T;V)
.

From Lemmas
4.1.28
and
4.1.30
and the Fréchet-differentiability of the mapping
({\mathscr{P}}^{\prime}\times{\mathscr{P}})\ni(f,p)\mapsto{\langle f,p\rangle}_{{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{P}}}
it follows that the Lagrangian is continuously Fréchet-differentiable in
\mathscr{X}\times\Lambda
. In particular, we infer from (
4.1.20
) that

\displaystyle\hat{J}^{\prime}(u)u^{\delta}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{y}(\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u,u,p)\mathcal{S}u^{\delta}+\mathcal{L}_{u}(\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u,u,p)u^{\delta}

(4.1.23)

\displaystyle={\langle\mathcal{L}_{y}(\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u,u,p),\mathcal{S}u^{\delta}\rangle}_{{\mathscr{Y}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{Y}}}+{\langle\mathcal{L}_{u}(\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u,u,p),u^{\delta}\rangle}_{{\mathscr{U}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{U}}}

for every
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
,
p\in{\mathscr{P}}
and
u^{\delta}\in{\mathscr{U}}
, where
\mathcal{L}_{y}
and
\mathcal{L}_{u}
denote the partial Fréchet derivatives with respect to
y
and
u
, respectively.

Let
x\in{\mathscr{X}}
be chosen arbitrarily. It follows from Lemma
4.1.30
that

\displaystyle e^{\prime}(x)=\big(e_{y}(x)\,\big|\,e_{u}(x)\big):{\mathscr{X}}\to{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime}

is a bounded linear operator, where the partial Fréchet-derivatives
e_{y}=(e_{1y},e_{2y}):X\to{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime}
and
e_{u}=(e_{1u},e_{2u}):{\mathscr{Y}}\to{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime}
are given as

\displaystyle{\langle e_{1y}^{\prime}(x)y^{\delta},p^{1}\rangle}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime}),L^{2}(0,T;V)}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}{\langle y^{\delta}_{t}(t),p^{1}(t)\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}+a(t;y^{\delta}(t),p^{1}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle e_{2y}^{\prime}(x)y^{\delta}

\displaystyle=y^{\delta}(0),

\displaystyle{\langle e_{1u}^{\prime}(x)u^{\delta},p^{1}\rangle}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime}),L^{2}(0,T;V)}

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{T}{\langle(\mathcal{B}u^{\delta})(t),p^{1}(t)\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\,\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle e_{2u}^{\prime}(x)u^{\delta}

\displaystyle=0

for
y^{\delta}\in{\mathscr{Y}}
,
u^{\delta}\in{\mathscr{U}}
and
p^{1}\in L^{2}(0,T;V)
.

In the following lemma, which is proved in Section
4.5.1
, it is proved that the linear operator
e_{y}(x)
possesses a bounded inverse for all
x\in{\mathscr{X}}
. This implies that the mapping
e
satisfies a constrained qualification condition; cf.
[
Lue69
,
MZ79
,
NW06
]
.

Lemma 4.1.31
.

Let Assumption
4.1.1
hold and
x=(y,u)\in{\mathscr{X}}
. Then the linear and bounded operator
e_{y}(x):{\mathscr{Y}}\to{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime}
is bijective with a bounded inverse
e_{y}(x)^{-1}
. In particular,
e^{\prime}(x)
is surjective.

Remark 4.1.32
.

Differentiating (
4.1.19
) with respect to
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
in direction
u^{\delta}\in{\mathscr{U}}
we find

\displaystyle e_{y}(\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u,u)\mathcal{S}u^{\delta}+e_{u}(\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u,u)u^{\delta}=0\quad\text{in }{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime}.

Since the inverse operator
e_{y}(x)^{-1}:{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime}\to{\mathscr{Y}}
exists and is bounded for every
x\in{\mathscr{X}}
, we infer that
y^{\delta}=\mathcal{S}u^{\delta}
satisfies

\displaystyle y^{\delta}=-e_{y}(\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u,u)^{-1}e_{u}(\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u,u)u^{\delta}\quad\text{in }{\mathscr{Y}}.

Moreover, if
\mathscr{J}_{\mathscr{P}}:{\mathscr{P}}\to{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime}
and
\mathscr{J}_{\mathscr{Y}}:{\mathscr{Y}}\to{\mathscr{Y}}^{\prime}
denote the Riesz isomorphisms, then the operator
\mathscr{J}_{\mathscr{P}}^{-1}e_{y}(x):{\mathscr{Y}}\to{\mathscr{P}}
is an isomorphism of Hilbert spaces, so it has a Hilbert adjoint
(\mathscr{J}_{\mathscr{P}}^{-1}e_{y}(x))^{\star}:{\mathscr{P}}\to{\mathscr{Y}}
which is likewise bijective with inverse
(\mathscr{J}_{\mathscr{P}}^{-1}e_{y}(x))^{\star,-1}:{\mathscr{Y}}\to{\mathscr{P}}
.

Lastly, since
{\mathscr{P}}
is a Hilbert space, it is reflexive and we can identify the
bidual space

({\mathscr{P}}^{\prime})^{\prime}
with
{\mathscr{P}}
itself. Therefore, the dual operator
e_{y}(x)^{\prime}
can be considered a mapping from
{\mathscr{P}}
to
{\mathscr{Y}}^{\prime}
. Furthermore, it is bijective and its the inverse is given by

\displaystyle\big(e_{y}(x)^{\prime}\big)^{-1}=(\mathscr{J}_{\mathscr{P}}^{-1}e_{y}(x))^{\star,-1}\mathscr{J}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{-1}:~{\mathscr{Y}}^{\prime}\to{\mathscr{P}}

which can be shown in a straightforward way.
\blacksquare

The next proposition yields a characterization of the first-order optimality condition
\mathcal{L}_{y}(x,p)=0
in
{\mathscr{Y}}^{\prime}
. For a proof we refer to Section
4.5.1
.

Proposition 4.1.33
.

Let Assumption
4.1.1
and
4.1.8
hold,
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
,
y(u)=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u\in{\mathscr{Y}}
and
x(u)=(y(u),u)\in{\mathscr{X}}
. Then

\mathscr{L}_{y}(x(u),p(u))=0\quad\text{in }{\mathscr{Y}}^{\prime}

(4.1.24)

is equivalent with

p(u)=\big(p^{1}(u),p^{2}(u)\big)=-\big(e_{y}(y(u),u)^{\prime}\big)^{-1}J_{y}(y(u),u)\in{\mathscr{P}}.

(4.1.25)

Moreover,
p=(p^{1},p^{2})
defined by (
4.1.25
) satisfies
p^{1}=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}u
and
p^{2}=p^{1}(0)
.

For now, let us quickly summarize what we have proven altogether for a variable
\bar{x}=(\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}\bar{u},\bar{u})
.

\blacksquare

Theorem
4.1.20
stated that
\bar{x}
solves the quadratic programming problem (
QP
) if and only if
\bar{u}\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
and

\displaystyle{\langle\nabla\hat{J}(\bar{u}),u-\bar{u}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}\geq 0\quad\text{for all }u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

\blacksquare

Associated with the primal variable
\bar{x}
the adjoint variable
\bar{p}
is defined like in (
4.1.25
) to
\bar{p}=-(e_{y}(\bar{x})^{\prime})^{-1}J_{y}(\bar{x})\in{\mathscr{P}}
. It was shown in Proposition
4.1.33
that this variable is given by
\bar{p}=(\bar{p}^{1},\bar{p}^{2})
with
\bar{p}^{1}=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}u
(the solution to the adjoint equation (
4.1.12
)) and
\bar{p}^{2}=\bar{p}^{1}(0)
.

\blacksquare

By Proposition
4.1.33
, we know that the variable
\bar{p}
also satisfies
\mathcal{L}_{y}(\bar{x},\bar{p})=0
in
{\mathscr{Y}}^{\prime}
. Inserting this into the derivative representation (
4.1.23
) yields
\hat{J}^{\prime}(\bar{u})=\mathcal{L}_{u}(\bar{x},\bar{p})
in
{\mathscr{U}}^{\prime}
.

We summarize the above observations in the following theorem.

Theorem 4.1.34
.

Let Assumption
4.1.1
and
4.1.8
hold. Then
\bar{x}=(\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}\bar{u},\bar{u})
is a solution to (
QP
) if and only if the first-order optimality system holds for
\bar{p}=(\bar{p}^{1},\bar{p}^{2})
with
\bar{p}^{1}=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}\bar{u}
and
\bar{p}^{2}=\bar{p}^{1}(0)
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{y}(\bar{x},\bar{p})=0\text{ in }{\mathscr{Y}}^{\prime},\quad\mathcal{L}_{u}(\bar{x},\bar{p})(u-\bar{u})\geq 0\text{ for all }u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{u}\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

4.1.5
Second-order derivatives

In this section we study second derivatives (see Definition
A.4.62
) of the cost functional and of the constraints.

Lemma 4.1.35
.

Let Assumption
4.1.1
be satisfied. Then
J:{\mathscr{X}}\to\mathbb{R}
is twice continuously Fréchet-differentiable in
{\mathscr{X}}
and

\displaystyle{\langle J^{\prime\prime}(x)x^{\delta},\tilde{x}^{\delta}\rangle}_{{\mathscr{X}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{X}}}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\int_{0}^{T}{\langle\tilde{y}^{\delta}(t),y^{\delta}(t)\rangle}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{2}\,{\langle\tilde{y}^{\delta}(T),y^{\delta}(T)\rangle}_{H}+\sigma\,{\langle\tilde{u}^{\delta},u^{\delta}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}

(4.1.26)

for
x=(y,u)\in{\mathscr{X}}
,
x^{\delta}=(y^{\delta},u^{\delta})\in{\mathscr{X}}
and
\tilde{x}^{\delta}=(\tilde{y}^{\delta},\tilde{u}^{\delta})\in{\mathscr{X}}
. Furthermore, the operator
e:{\mathscr{X}}\to{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime}
is twice continuously Fréchet-differentiable and its second derivative is zero.

For a proof of Lemma
4.1.35
we refer to Section
4.5.1
. It follows from the lemma that the Lagrange function
\mathcal{L}
is twice continuously Fréchet-differentiable. In particular, we find that

{\langle\mathcal{L}_{xx}(x,p)x^{\delta},x^{\delta}\rangle}_{{\mathscr{X}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{X}}}=\sigma_{1}\int_{0}^{T}{\|y^{\delta}(t)\|}^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{2}\,{\|y^{\delta}(T)\|}_{H}^{2}+\sigma\,{\|u^{\delta}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}

(4.1.27)

for
x=(y,u)\in{\mathscr{X}}
,
p\in{\mathscr{P}}
and
x^{\delta}=(y^{\delta},u^{\delta})\in{\mathscr{X}}
. Recall that for every
x\in{\mathscr{X}}
, the kernel of
e^{\prime}(x)
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{ker}\,e^{\prime}(x)=\big\{x^{\delta}\in{\mathscr{X}}\,\big|\,e^{\prime}(x)x^{\delta}=0\text{ in }{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime}\big\};

cf. Definition
A.2.42
. Elements in
\mathrm{ker}\,e^{\prime}(x)
can be characterized as follows.

Lemma 4.1.36
.

Let Assumption
4.1.1
hold. Suppose that
x=(y,u)\in{\mathscr{X}}
and
x^{\delta}=(y^{\delta},u^{\delta})\in\mathrm{ker}\,e^{\prime}(x)\subset{\mathscr{X}}
. Then

{\|y^{\delta}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq c_{\mathsf{ker}}\,{\|u^{\delta}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}

(4.1.28)

for a constant
c_{\mathsf{ker}}>0
.

For a proof of Lemma
4.1.36
we refer to Section
4.5.1
. Estimate (
4.1.28
) can be utilized to proof the
second-order sufficient optimality condition
for (
QP
). This is stated in the next proposition which is proved in Section
4.5.1
.

Proposition 4.1.37
.

Let Assumptions
4.1.1
and
4.1.8
hold. Suppose that
\sigma>0
is satisfied. Then the second Fréchet derivative
\mathcal{L}_{xx}(x,p)
with respect to
x
is coercive on
\mathrm{ker}\,e^{\prime}(x)
for all
x\in{\mathscr{X}}
and
p\in{\mathscr{P}}
, i.e., there exists a constant
\kappa>0
such that

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{L}_{xx}(x,p)x^{\delta},x^{\delta}\rangle}_{{\mathscr{X}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{X}}}\geq\kappa\,{\|x^{\delta}\|}_{\mathscr{X}}^{2}\quad\text{for all }x^{\delta}\in\mathrm{ker}\,e^{\prime}(x)

is valid.

Remark 4.1.38
.

Let Assumptions
4.1.1
and
4.1.8
hold. Suppose that
\bar{x}=(\bar{y},\bar{u})
and
\bar{p}=(\bar{p}^{1},\bar{p}^{2})
solve the first order system

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{y}(\bar{x},\bar{p})=0\text{ in }{\mathscr{Y}}^{\prime},\quad\mathcal{L}_{u}(\bar{x},\bar{p})(u-\bar{u})\geq 0\text{ for all }u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{u}\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

Then we infer from Proposition
4.1.37
that
\bar{x}
satisfies a second-order sufficient optimality condition for (
QP
). In particular,
\bar{x}
is the unique global solution to (
QP
).
\blacksquare

4.2
The continuous POD method for the optimal control problem

In this section, we introduce POD Galerkin approximations to the state and adjoint variables in the optimal control problem (
P
). The linear state and adjoint solution operators
\mathcal{S}
,
\mathcal{A}
from the previous section are then replaced by lower-dimensional counterparts
\mathcal{S}^{\ell}
,
\mathcal{A}^{\ell}
. We study the convergence of these POD discretizations, where we make use of the analysis in
[
HV08
,
KV01
,
KV02b
,
KV02a
,
Sin14
,
TV09
]
. For a general introduction we also refer the reader to the survey papers
[
BSV14
,
HV05
,
SV10
]
.

4.2.1
POD Galerkin schemes for the dual equation

Most numerical solution strategies for (
P
) rely on repeatedly solving systems like the state equation
y=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u
or the adjoint equation
p=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}u
. If the underlying spaces
H
,
V
are highly-dimensional, this requires a lot of computational effort. To work around this problem, we can compute a POD basis
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell
and then perform a Galerkin projection onto the according POD space. As a result, the high-dimensional problem (
P
) is replaced by a lower-dimensional one. In this section, we we will perform this projection and discuss consequences for the primal and dual variables.

Recall that in (
2.4.6a
) and (
2.4.6b
) we have defined the projections

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}:H\to H^{\ell},

\displaystyle v^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}\varphi

\displaystyle\text{solves }\min_{w^{\ell}\in H^{\ell}}{\|\varphi-w^{\ell}\|}_{H}\quad\text{ for }\varphi\in H,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}:H\to V^{\ell},

\displaystyle v^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}\varphi

\displaystyle\text{solves }\min_{w^{\ell}\in V^{\ell}}{\|\varphi-w^{\ell}\|}_{H}\quad\text{ for }\varphi\in H.

We require some data trajectories
\{y^{k}(t)\,|\,0\leq t\leq T\}
(
1\leq k\leq K
) to compute a POD basis. This is done as follows:

1)

Choose an admissible control
u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
and compute the solutions
y=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u
and
p=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}u
to (
4.1.2
) and (
4.1.12
), respectively. We assume that
y,p\in H^{1}(0,T;V)
holds which implies that
y,p\in H^{1}(0,T;X)
.

2)

Set
K=2
,
\omega_{i}^{K}=1
for
i=1,2
,
y^{1}=y
and
y^{2}=p
, i.e., the
snapshot space
is given as

\mathscr{V}=\mathrm{span}\,\bigg\{\int_{0}^{T}w_{1}(t)y(t)+w_{2}(t)p(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\,\Big|\,w_{1},w_{2}\in L^{2}(0,T)\bigg\}\subset X

(4.2.1)

with the dimension

\displaystyle d=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\dim\mathscr{V}&&\text{if }\dim\mathscr{V}<\infty,\\
&\infty&&\text{otherwise}.\end{aligned}\right.

3)

For any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
with
\ell\leq d
solve (cf.

P
ℓ

H

)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\min\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\Big\|y^{k}(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y^{k}(t),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{H}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 1.42262pt\text{s.t. }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset H\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{H}=\delta_{ij},~1\leq i,j\leq\ell\end{aligned}\right.

(
\mathbf{P}^{\ell}_{H}
)

or

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\min\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\Big\|y^{k}(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y^{k}(t),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{V}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 1.42262pt\text{s.t. }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset V\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{V}=\delta_{ij},~1\leq i,j\leq\ell.\end{aligned}\right.

(
\mathbf{P}^{\ell}_{V}
)

to obtain POD bases
\{\psi_{i}^{H}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
or
\{\psi^{V}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
, respectively, with rank
\ell
. According to the a-priori error estimates (cf. (
3.4.27
)) the number
\ell
should be chosen as follows: for given tolerance
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{POD}}>0

\displaystyle\xi\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{H}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{H}\big\|_{V}^{2}

\displaystyle<\varepsilon_{\mathsf{POD}}\text{ if }X=H\quad\text{and}\quad\xi\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{V}\,{\|\psi_{i}^{V}-\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}\psi_{i}^{V}\|}_{V}^{2}<\varepsilon_{\mathsf{POD}}\text{ if }X=V

with a factor
\xi\in(0,1)
.

4)

Set
H^{\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\psi_{1}^{H},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}^{H}\big\}
or
V^{\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\psi_{1}^{V},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}^{V}\big\}
.

In other words, we choose the data trajectories for the POD basis as the primal and dual variables
y
and
p
belonging to a particular control
u
. As long as an optimization algorithm remains in close proximity to this control, the POD surrogate model will usually be a good approximation of the original problem.

Remark 4.2.1
.

1)

Recall that the pairs
\{(\lambda_{i}^{H},\psi_{i}^{H})\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
and
\{(\lambda_{i}^{V},\psi_{i}^{V})\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
satisfy the eigenvalue problems (
3.3.21a
) and (
3.3.21b
), respectively.

2)

From Lemma
2.4.6
-1) it follows that
H^{\ell}\subset V
holds true.

3)

If we do not distinguish between the choice
X=H
or
X=V
we only write
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
for the computed POD basis of rank
\ell
and define the subspace
X^{\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}\big\}\subset V
.

4)

Note that we do not include time derivatives in our snapshot set. Of course, we can also enlarge the snapshot space by setting
\wp=4
,
\omega_{i}^{\wp}=1
for
i=1,\ldots,4
,
y^{1}=y
,
y^{2}=y_{t}
,
y^{3}=p
and
y^{4}=p_{t}
, i.e., the snapshot space is given as

\displaystyle\mathscr{V}=\bigg\{\sum_{k=1}^{4}\int_{0}^{T}\phi_{k}(t)y^{k}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\in X\Big|\phi_{k}\in L^{2}(0,T)\text{ for }k=1,\dots,4\bigg\}.

In that case the obtained rates of a-priori convergence results are based on Theorem
3.3.14
.
\blacksquare

Recall that in (
2.4.6a
) and (
2.4.6b
) we have defined the projections

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}:H\to H^{\ell},

\displaystyle v^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}\varphi

\displaystyle\text{solves }\min_{w^{\ell}\in H^{\ell}}{\|\varphi-w^{\ell}\|}_{H}\quad\text{ for }\varphi\in H,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}:H\to V^{\ell},

\displaystyle v^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}\varphi

\displaystyle\text{solves }\min_{w^{\ell}\in V^{\ell}}{\|\varphi-w^{\ell}\|}_{H}\quad\text{ for }\varphi\in H.

We will utilize the results presented in Proposition
2.4.13
and Section
3.3.2
. For the readers convenience we sum up the required hypotheses in the next

Assumption 4.2.2
.

1)

Let Assumptions
4.1.1
and
4.1.8
hold. Moreover,
y=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u
and
p=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}u
belong to the space
H^{1}(0,T;V)
for any
u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
.

2)

The Hilbert space
X
denotes either
H
or
V
. In case of
X=V
we suppose that
y_{\circ}\in V
is valid.

3)

We set
K=2
,
\omega_{1}^{K}=\omega_{2}^{K}=1
and
y^{1}=y
,
y^{2}=p
. The snapshot space
\mathscr{V}
is given by (
4.2.1
) with dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. If
X=H
is chosen, we suppose that
d\in\mathbb{N}
is satisfied, i.e.,
\lambda_{i}^{H}=0
for
i>d
. For
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
with
\ell\leq d
let
\{\psi_{i}^{H}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
and
\{\psi_{i}^{V}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
be solutions to (

P
ℓ

H

) and (

P
ℓ

V

), respectively.

4)

In case of
X=H
, let
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
denote
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}
. If
X=V
, let it be given by
\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}
.

The POD Galerkin scheme for the state equation (
4.1.2
) has already been introduced in Section
3.3.1
. If
y=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u
denotes the full-order state variable, its POD-Galerkin approximation is defined as
y^{\ell}=\hat{y}^{\ell}+\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u
and solves

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle y^{\ell}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;y^{\ell}(t),\psi)

\displaystyle={\langle(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(t),\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\quad\text{for all }\psi\in X^{\ell}\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

(4.2.2a)

\displaystyle y^{\ell}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y_{\circ},

(4.2.2b)

where
\hat{y}^{\ell}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u
solve (
3.3.10
) and (
3.3.11
), respectively. The operator
\mathcal{S}^{\ell}:{\mathscr{U}}\to H^{1}(0,T;V)
was shown in Corollary
3.3.9
to be linear and continuous. If Assumption
4.2.2
hold, we can infer from
\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y_{\circ}\|_{H}\leq\|y_{\circ}\|_{H}
for all
y_{\circ}\in H
and from Theorem
3.3.5
that

{\|y^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq C_{y}\left({\|y_{\circ}\|}_{H}+{\|\mathcal{F}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}+{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\right)

(4.2.3)

for a constant
C_{y}>0
which is independent of
\ell
. Combining Theorems
3.3.14
and
3.3.17
, there also exists a constant
C>0
satisfying the a-priori error estimate

\displaystyle{\|y-y^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}\leq C\cdot\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{H}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{H}\big\|_{V}^{2},&&\text{for }X=H\\
&\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{V}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{V}-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\psi_{i}^{V}\big\|_{V}^{2},&&\text{for }X=V.\end{aligned}\right.

In this subsection, we will perform a analogous Galerkin projection for the adjoint equation (
4.1.12
) which was used to express the gradient of the function
\hat{J}
. First of all, we make the ansatz

p^{\ell}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{p}_{i}^{\ell}(t)\psi_{i}\in X^{\ell},\quad t\in[0,T],

(4.2.4)

and replace
y,p
by
y^{\ell},p^{\ell}
in (
4.1.12
). Second, we consider the equation (
4.1.12a
) in
(X^{\ell})^{\prime}
instead of
V^{\prime}
and project the terminal data in (
4.1.12b
) onto
X^{\ell}
which leads to the following problem:

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle p^{\ell}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\psi,p^{\ell}(t))

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\,{\langle{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)-y^{\ell}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}\quad\text{for all }\psi\in X^{\ell}\text{ a.e. in }[0,T),

(4.2.5a)

\displaystyle p^{\ell}(T)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}^{\ell}{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-y^{\ell}(T)\big).

(4.2.5b)

Note that
\tilde{p}^{\ell}(t)=p^{\ell}(T-t)
,
t\in[0,T]
, satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle\tilde{p}^{\ell}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}+\tilde{a}(t;\psi,\tilde{p}^{\ell}(t))

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\,{\langle{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)-y^{\ell}(T-t),\psi\rangle}_{H}\quad\text{for all }\psi\in X^{\ell}\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

(4.2.6a)

\displaystyle\tilde{p}^{\ell}(T)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}^{\ell}{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-y^{\ell}(T)\big).

(4.2.6b)

We can observe that (
4.2.6
) has a very similar structure to (
4.2.2
), so existence and uniqueness of solutions along with a boundedness result in the manner of (
4.2.3
) can be shown exactly as the proof of Theorem
3.3.5
, which gives the following result.

Proposition 4.2.3
.

Let Assumptions
4.2.2
hold. Then for every
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
there exists a unique
p^{\ell}\in H^{1}(0,T;X^{\ell})\hookrightarrow{\mathscr{Y}}
to (
4.2.5
) satisfying

\displaystyle{\|p^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq C\big(\sigma_{2}\,{\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-y^{\ell}(T)\|}_{H}+\sigma_{1}\,{\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}-y^{\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}\big)

for a constant
C\geq 0
which is independent of
\ell
and for
y^{\ell}=\hat{y}^{\ell}+\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u
.

The following corollary follows directly from Proposition
4.2.3
. For more details we refer to Section
4.5.2
.

Corollary 4.2.4
.

Let all assumptions of Proposition
4.2.3
hold.

1)

There exists a unique solution
\hat{p}^{\ell}\in H^{1}(0,T;X^{\ell})\hookrightarrow{\mathscr{Y}}
to

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle\hat{p}^{\ell}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\psi,\hat{p}^{\ell}(t))

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\,{\langle{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)-\hat{y}^{\ell}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}\quad\text{for all }\psi\in X^{\ell}\text{ a.e. in }[0,T),

\displaystyle\hat{p}^{\ell}(T)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}^{\ell}{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-\hat{y}^{\ell}(T)\big).

In particular, we have

\displaystyle{\|\hat{p}^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq C\big(\sigma_{2}\,{\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-\hat{y}^{\ell}(T)\|}_{H}+\sigma_{1}\,{\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}-\hat{y}^{\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}\big)

for a constant
C\geq 0
which is independent of
\ell
.

2)

Let us introduce the linear operator
\mathcal{A}^{\ell}:{\mathscr{U}}\to{\mathscr{Y}}
as follows:
p^{\ell}=\mathcal{A}^{\ell}u
,
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
, satisfies

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle p^{\ell}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\psi,p^{\ell}(t))

\displaystyle=-\sigma_{1}\,{\langle(\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u)(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}\quad\text{for all }\psi\in X^{\ell}\text{ a.e. in }[0,T),

\displaystyle p^{\ell}(T)

\displaystyle=-\sigma_{2}(\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u)(T).

Then
\mathcal{A}^{\ell}
is well-defined and bounded. In particular,

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{A}^{\ell}u\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq C\big(\sigma_{2}\,{\|(\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u)(T)\|}_{H}+\sigma_{1}\,{\|\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}\big)

(4.2.7)

for a constant
C\geq 0
which is independent of
\ell
.

Remark 4.2.5
.

1)

The solution to (
4.2.5
) can be expressed as
p^{\ell}=\hat{p}^{\ell}+\mathcal{A}^{\ell}u
.

2)

The equation (
4.2.5a
) can be expressed as an
\ell
-dimensional system of ordinary differential equations. For that purpose we introduce the
\ell
-dimensional vectors

\displaystyle\mathrm{p}^{\ell}(t)=\big(\mathrm{p}_{i}^{\ell}(t)\big)_{1\leq i\leq\ell},\quad\mathrm{y}_{1}^{\ell}(t)=\big({\langle{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{H}\big)_{1\leq i\leq\ell},\quad\mathrm{y}_{2}^{\ell}=\big(\mathrm{y}_{2i}^{\ell}\big)_{1\leq i\leq\ell},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\ell}{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{y}_{2i}^{\ell}\psi_{i}\in X^{\ell}.

Recall that the matrices
{\bm{M}}^{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell\times\ell}
and
{\bm{A}}^{\ell}(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell\times\ell}
have been defined in (
3.3.5
). Then inserting (
4.2.4
) as well as (
3.3.4
) into (
4.2.5a
) and choosing
\psi=\psi_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,\ell
, we derive

-{\bm{M}}^{\ell}\dot{\mathrm{p}}^{\ell}(t)+{\bm{A}}^{\ell}(t)^{\top}\mathrm{p}^{\ell}(t)=\sigma_{1}\big(\mathrm{y}_{1}^{\ell}(t)-{\bm{M}}^{\ell}\mathrm{y}^{\ell}(t)\big),\quad t\in[0,T).

(4.2.8a)

Moreover, (
4.2.5b
) implies that

\mathrm{p}^{\ell}(T)=\sigma_{2}\big(\mathrm{y}_{2}^{\ell}-\mathrm{y}^{\ell}(T)\big)

(4.2.8b)

holds true.
\blacksquare

In the next corollary we present an a-priori bound for
p^{\ell}
. The proof is given in Section
4.5.2
.

Corollary 4.2.6
.

Let all assumptions of Proposition
4.2.3
hold. Then for
p^{\ell}=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}^{\ell}(u)
it holds

\displaystyle{\|p^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq C_{p}\,\big({\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}+{\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}+{\|y_{\circ}\|}_{H}+{\|f\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}+{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\big)

for a constant
C_{p}\geq 0
which is independent of
\ell
.

4.2.2
POD a-priori error analysis for the dual equation

By Assumption
4.2.2
-4),
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
is either given by
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}
or
\mathcal{Q}_{H}
. Therefore, it is always an
H
-orthonormal projection. Our next goal is to derive a-priori error estimates for the term

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}{\|p(t)-p^{\ell}(t)\|}_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t,

where
p
and
p^{\ell}
are the solutions to (
4.1.12
) and (
4.2.5
), respectively. We suppose that
p\in H^{1}(0,T;V)
holds. Recall that
X^{\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\{\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}\}\subset V
for the two choices
X=H
and
X=V
. Analogously to Section
3.3.2
we will make use of the following decomposition (cf. (
3.3.12
))

\displaystyle p(t)-p^{\ell}(t)=p(t)-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p(t)+\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p(t)-p^{\ell}(t)=\rho^{\ell}(t)+\theta^{\ell}(t)

with
\rho^{\ell}(t)=p(t)-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p(t)\in(X^{\ell})^{\bot}
and
\theta^{\ell}(t)=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p(t)-p^{\ell}(t)\in X^{\ell}
. Note that
(X^{\ell})^{\perp}
denotes the orthogonal space to
X^{\ell}
with respect to the
H
-inner product because
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
is an
H
-orthogonal projection. The above decomposition gives

\int_{0}^{T}{\|p(t)-p^{\ell}(t)\|}_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq 2\int_{0}^{T}{\|\rho^{\ell}(t)\|}_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+2\int_{0}^{T}{\|\theta^{\ell}(t)\|}_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t.

(4.2.9)

Lemma 4.2.7
.

Let Assumption
4.2.2
hold. Let
\theta^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p-p^{\ell}
, where
p
and
p^{\ell}
are the solutions to (
4.1.12
) and (
4.2.5
), respectively. Then

{\|\theta^{\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}\leq c_{\theta}\Big({\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p-p\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}+{\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y-y\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}\Big)

(4.2.10)

for a constant
c_{\theta}>0
which is independent of
\ell
.

Theorem 4.2.8
.

Assume that Assumption
4.2.2
hold. Let
\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{HV^{\ell}}\|_{\mathscr{L}(V)}
be bounded independently of
\ell
. Suppose that
p
and
p^{\ell}
are the solutions to (
4.1.12
) and (
4.2.5
), respectively. Then there exists a constant
C>0
satisfying the a-priori error estimate

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\big\|p(t)-p^{\ell}(t)\big\|_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\cdot\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{H}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{H}\big\|_{V}^{2},&&\text{for }X=H,\\
&\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{V}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{V}-\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}\psi_{i}^{V}\big\|_{V}^{2},&&\text{for }X=V.\end{aligned}\right.

Remark 4.2.9
.

Let us discuss variants of the a-priori error estimate for the dual variable provided different snapshots are chosen.

1)

In Assumption
4.2.2
-3) we could also choose
K=1
,
\omega_{1}^{K}=1
and

\displaystyle\mathscr{V}=\mathrm{span}\,\bigg\{\int_{0}^{T}\phi(t)y(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\,\big|\,\phi\in L^{2}(0,T)\bigg\}\subset X,

i.e., the adjoint variable
p
is not included in the snapshot space. Then we only get the a-priori error estimates

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\big\|p(t)-p^{\ell}(t)\big\|_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\bigg({\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p-p\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}+\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{H}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{H}\big\|_{V}^{2}\bigg)

for
X=H
and

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\big\|p(t)-p^{\ell}(t)\big\|_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\bigg({\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p-p\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}+\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{V}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{V}-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\psi_{i}^{V}\big\|_{V}^{2}\bigg)

for
X=V
. Thus, the inclusion of adjoint information into the snapshot ensemble improves the approximation quality. This is also observed for nonlinear problems; see
[
DV01
]
.

2)

Now we choose
X=V
,
K=4
,
y^{1}=y
,
y^{2}=y_{t}
,
y^{3}=p
,
y^{4}=p_{t}
,
\omega_{k}^{K}=1
for
k=1,\ldots,4
and

\displaystyle\mathscr{V}=\mathrm{span}\,\bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{4}\int_{0}^{T}\phi_{k}(t)y^{k}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\,\Big|\,\phi_{k}\in L^{2}(0,T)\text{ for }k=1,\ldots,4\bigg\}\subset X,

i.e. the time derivatives are included in the snapshot space. Moreover, we take the projection
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}
with

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}:V\to V^{\ell},\quad v^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}\varphi\text{ solves }\min_{w^{\ell}\in V^{\ell}}{\|\varphi-w^{\ell}\|}_{V}\quad\text{ for }\varphi\in V.

Then the following a-priori error estimate (cf. Theorem
3.3.14
)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\big\|y(t)-y^{\ell}(t)\big\|_{V}^{2}+\big\|p(t)-p^{\ell}(t)\big\|_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\sum\limits_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{V}

holds for a constant
C>0
. Consequently, the rate of convergence for the state and adjoint variable is given by the decay of the eigenvalues.
\blacksquare

For every
u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
we set
p=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}u
and
p^{\ell}=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}^{\ell}u
. We infer from Theorem
4.2.8

{\|p-p^{\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}={\|(\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{A}^{\ell})u\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}\to 0\quad\text{for }\ell\to\infty.

(4.2.11)

Recall that we have introduced the linear, bounded operators
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\ell}
in (
4.1.10
) and Corollary
4.2.4
-2, respectively. We give sufficient conditions that
\mathcal{A}^{\ell}
converges to
\mathcal{A}
in
\mathscr{L}({\mathscr{U}},{\mathscr{Y}})
as
\ell\to\infty
. The proof of the result can be shown as in the proof of Theorem
3.3.18
.

Theorem 4.2.10
.

Let Assumptions
4.1.1
,
4.1.8
and
4.2.2
hold. Assume that
\|\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}\|_{\mathscr{L}(V)}
is bounded independently of
\ell
. We suppose that either
{\mathscr{U}}
is a finite-dimensional Hilbert space or that the operator
\mathcal{A}
is even compact. Moreover, let one of the following assumptions be satisfied:

1)

X=H
,
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}
,
\lambda_{i}^{H}>0
for all
i\in\mathbb{I}
and
y(t)
,
p(t)\in\mathrm{ran}\,(\mathcal{Y})\subset V
f.a.a.
t\in[0,T]
, where the linear and bounded operator
\mathcal{Y}:L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{2})\to X
is given as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}\phi=\int_{0}^{T}\phi^{1}(t)y(t,\cdot)+\phi^{2}(t)p(t,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}t\quad\text{for }\phi=(\phi^{1},\phi^{2})\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{2});

2)

X=H
,
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}
and
\lambda_{i}^{H}>0
holds for all
i\in\mathbb{I}
;

3)

X=V
,
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{V}
and
\lambda_{i}^{V}>0
holds for all
i\in\mathbb{I}
.

Then
\|\mathcal{A}^{\ell}-\mathcal{A}\|_{\mathscr{L}({\mathscr{U}},{\mathscr{Y}})}\to 0
as
\ell\to\infty
.

4.2.3
POD Galerkin scheme for the optimality system

In this section, we will present the POD Galerkin approximation for the optimal control problem (

^
P

). It is defined as follows:

\min\hat{J}^{\ell}(u)\quad\text{s.t.}\quad u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}},

(
\mathbf{\hat{P}}^{\ell}
)

where the cost is given by
\hat{J}^{\ell}(u)=J(\hat{y}^{\ell}+\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u,u)
for
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
. Due to its similar structure, many results for (

^
P

) from Section
4.1
translate to (

^
P

ℓ

) and the proofs are almost identical. Therefore, we will leave it to the interested reader to proof the results below as an exercise; cf. Theorem
4.1.16
, Lemma
4.1.24
and Remark
4.1.25
.

Theorem 4.2.11
.

Let Assumption
4.2.2
be satisfied.

1)

Problem (

^
P

ℓ

) possesses a solution
\bar{u}
. If
\sigma_{1}>0
or
\sigma>0
the solution is unique.

2)

Let
\Theta:W_{0}(0,T)\to W_{0}(0,T))^{\prime}
and
\Xi:L^{2}(0,T;H)\times H\to W_{0}(0,T)^{\prime}
by given by (
4.1.13
). Then it holds

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\mathcal{A}^{\ell}=-(\mathcal{S}^{\ell})^{\prime}\Theta\mathcal{S}^{\ell}\text{ in }L({\mathscr{U}}),\quad\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\hat{p}=(\mathcal{S}^{\ell})^{\prime}\Xi\big(y_{1}^{\mathsf{d}}-\hat{y}^{\ell},y_{2}^{\mathsf{d}}-\hat{y}^{\ell}(T)\big)\text{ in }{\mathscr{U}}.

3)
