The gradient of
\hat{J}^{\ell}
is given by

\displaystyle\nabla\hat{J}^{\ell}(u)=-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}p^{\ell}+\sigma(u-\tilde{u}^{\mathsf{n}})\quad\text{with }p^{\ell}=\mathcal{A}^{\ell}u+\hat{p}^{\ell}.

We will assume that (

^
P

ℓ

) admits a unique solution
\bar{u}^{\ell}
. Similar to the non-reduced case, a first-order sufficient optimality condition is given by the variational inequality

{\langle\sigma(\bar{u}^{\ell}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p}^{\ell},u-\bar{u}^{\ell}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}\geq 0\quad\text{for all }u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}

(4.2.12)

with
\bar{p}^{\ell}=\hat{p}^{\ell}+\mathcal{A}^{\ell}\bar{u}^{\ell}
. The next theorem we present an a-priori result for the control variable. Its proof is given in Section
4.5.2
.

Theorem 4.2.12
.

Assume that Assumptions
4.2.2
and that
\bar{u}
is the solution to (

^
P

). Let
\|\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}\|_{\mathscr{L}(V)}
be bounded independently of
\ell
. Furthermore,
\bar{u}^{\ell}
denotes the unique solution to (
4.2.12
). Then there exists a constant
C>0
depending on
c_{V}
,
\gamma
,
\gamma_{1}
,
T
,
\sigma_{1}
,
\sigma_{2}
and the norm
\|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(0,T;V),{\mathscr{U}})}
such that the a-priori error estimate

\big\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\big\|_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}\leq C\cdot\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{H}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{H}\big\|_{V}^{2}&&\text{for }X=H,\\
&\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{V}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{V}-\mathcal{Q}_{H}^{\ell}\psi_{i}^{V}\big\|_{V}^{2}&&\text{for }X=V\end{aligned}\right.

(4.2.13)

holds.

Remark 4.2.13
.

From Remark
4.2.9
we obtain the following consequences:

1)

Instead of Assumption
4.2.2
-2) let us choose
K=1
,
\omega_{1}^{K}=1
and

\displaystyle\mathscr{V}=\mathrm{span}\,\bigg\{\int_{0}^{T}\phi(t)y(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\,\Big|\,\phi\in L^{2}(0,T)\bigg\}\subset X

i.e., the adjoint variable
p
is not included in the snapshot space. Then we only get the a-priori error estimates

\displaystyle{\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}\leq C\bigg({\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p-p\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}+\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{H}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{H}\big\|_{V}^{2}\bigg)

for
X=H
and

\displaystyle{\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}\leq C\bigg({\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p-p\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}+\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{V}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{V}-\mathcal{Q}_{H}^{\ell}\psi_{i}^{V}\big\|_{V}^{2}\bigg)

for
X=V
. Thus, the inclusion of adjoint information into the snapshot ensemble improves the approximation quality for the control.

2)

Now we choose
X=V
,
K=4
,
y^{1}=y
,
y^{2}=y_{t}
,
y^{3}=p
,
y^{4}=p_{t}
,
\omega_{k}^{K}=1
for
k=1,\ldots,4
and

\displaystyle\mathscr{V}=\mathrm{span}\,\bigg\{\sum_{k=1}^{4}\int_{0}^{T}\phi_{k}(t)y^{k}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\,\Big|\,\phi_{k}\in L^{2}(0,T)\text{ for }k=1,\ldots,4\bigg\}\subset X,

i.e. the time derivatives are included in the snapshot space. Then (cf. Theorem
3.3.14
)

\displaystyle{\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}\leq C\sum\limits_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{V}

for a constant
C>0
and
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{V}
. Thus, the rate of convergence for the control variable is given by the decay of the eigenvalues.
\blacksquare

4.2.4
POD a-posteriori error analysis for the optimality system

In this section, we fix the more general control space
{\mathscr{U}}=L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{m})
. All results transfer in a natural way to the simpler case of
{\mathscr{U}}=\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

In
[
TV09
]
, POD a-posteriori error estimates are presented which can be applied to our optimal control problem as well. Based on a perturbation method (cf.
[
DHPY95
]
) it is deduced how far the suboptimal control
\bar{u}^{\ell}
is from the (unknown) exact optimal control
\bar{u}
. Thus, our goal is to estimate the norm
\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\|_{\mathscr{U}}
without the knowledge of the optimal solution
\bar{u}
. In general,
\bar{u}^{\ell}\neq\bar{u}
holds, so that
\bar{u}^{\ell}
does not satisfy the variational inequality (
4.1.17b
). However, we can define a function
\zeta^{\ell}\in{\mathscr{U}}
such that

{\langle\sigma(\bar{u}^{\ell}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\tilde{p}^{\ell}+\zeta^{\ell},u-\bar{u}^{\ell}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}\geq 0\quad\text{for all }u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}},

(4.2.14)

with
\tilde{p}^{\ell}=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}\bar{u}^{\ell}
. Therefore,
\bar{u}^{\ell}
satisfies the optimality condition of the perturbed parabolic optimal control problem

\displaystyle\min_{u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}}\tilde{J}(u)=J(\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u,u)+{\langle\zeta^{\ell},u\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}

with “perturbation”
\zeta^{\ell}
. The smaller
\zeta^{\ell}
is, the closer
\bar{u}^{\ell}
is to
\bar{u}
. Next we estimate
\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\|_{\mathscr{U}}
in terms of
\|\zeta^{\ell}\|_{\mathscr{U}}
. By Lemma
4.1.24
-2) we have

\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\big(\bar{p}-\tilde{p}^{\ell}\big)=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\mathcal{A}\big(\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\big)=-\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\Theta\mathcal{S}\big(\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\big)=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\Theta\big(\tilde{y}^{\ell}-\bar{y}\big)

(4.2.15)

with
\tilde{y}^{\ell}=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}\bar{u}^{\ell}
. Choosing
u=\bar{u}^{\ell}
in (
4.1.17b
),
u=\bar{u}
in (
4.2.14
) and using (
4.2.15
) we obtain

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq{\langle-\sigma(\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell})+\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\bar{p}-\tilde{p}^{\ell})+\zeta^{\ell},\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}

\displaystyle=-\sigma\,{\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\|}^{2}_{\mathscr{U}}+{\langle\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\Theta(\tilde{y}^{\ell}-\bar{y}),\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}+{\langle\zeta^{\ell},\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}

\displaystyle=-\sigma\,{\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\|}^{2}_{\mathscr{U}}-{\langle\Theta(\bar{y}-\tilde{y}^{\ell}),\bar{y}-\tilde{y}^{\ell}\rangle}_{W_{0}(0,T)^{\prime},W_{0}(0,T)}+{\langle\zeta^{\ell},\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}

\displaystyle\leq-\sigma\,{\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\|}^{2}_{\mathscr{U}}+{\langle\zeta^{\ell},\bar{u}^{\ell}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}\leq-\sigma\,{\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\|}^{2}_{\mathscr{U}}+{\|\zeta^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}{\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}.

Hence, we get the a-posteriori error estimation

\displaystyle{\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\leq\frac{1}{\sigma}\,{\|\zeta^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}.

This estimate is stated in the following theorem, where, in addition, an appropriate perturbation function
\zeta^{\ell}
is chosen. For the proof we refer to Section
4.5.2
.

Theorem 4.2.14
.

Suppose that Assumption
4.2.2
holds. Let
\hat{y},\hat{p}\in H^{1}(0,T;V)
and
u\in{\mathscr{U}}=L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{m})
be arbitrarily given so that
\mathcal{S}u,\,\mathcal{A}u\in H^{1}(0,T;V)\setminus\{0\}
. To compute a POD basis
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell
we choose
\wp=2
,
y^{1}=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u
and
y^{2}=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}u
. Define the function
\zeta^{\ell}\in{\mathscr{U}}
by

\displaystyle\zeta_{i}^{\ell}(t)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &-\min(0,\xi_{i}^{\ell}(s))&&\text{a.e. in }\mathscr{A}^{\ell}_{\mathsf{a}i}=\big\{s\in\mathscr{D}\,|\bar{u}^{\ell}_{i}(s)=u_{\mathsf{a}i}(s)\big\},\\
&-\max(0,\xi^{\ell}_{i}(s))&&\text{a.e. in }\mathscr{A}^{\ell}_{\mathsf{b}i}=\big\{s\in\mathscr{D}\,|\bar{u}^{\ell}_{i}(s)=u_{\mathsf{b}i}(s)\big\},\\
&-\xi_{i}^{\ell}(t)&&\text{a.e. in }\mathscr{D}\setminus\big(\mathscr{A}^{\ell}_{\mathsf{a}i}\cup\mathscr{A}_{\mathsf{b}i}^{\ell}\big),\end{aligned}\right.

where
\xi^{\ell}=\sigma(\bar{u}^{\ell}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}\bar{u}^{\ell})
in
{\mathscr{U}}
. Then the a-posteriori error estimate

{\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\leq\frac{1}{\sigma}\,{\|\zeta^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}

(4.2.16)

holds. In particular,
\lim\limits_{\ell\to\infty}\big\|\zeta^{\ell}\big\|_{\mathscr{U}}=0
.

Remark 4.2.15
.

1)

Theorem
4.2.14
shows that
\|\zeta^{\ell}\|_{\mathscr{U}}
can be expected smaller than any tolerance
\epsilon>0
provided that
\ell
is taken sufficiently large. Motivated by this result we set up Algorithm 4.2.1. Note that the quality of the POD Galerkin scheme is improved by only increasing the number of POD basis elements. Another approach is to update the POD basis in the optimization process; see, e.g.,
[
AH02
,
AFS00
,
KV08
]
.

2)

In
[
SV13
]
POD a-posteriori error estimates are tested numerically for a linear-quadratic optimal control problem. It turns out that in certain cases only an increase of the number of POD ansatz functions does not decrease the error in the reduced-order solution satisfyingly. In this case a change of the POD basis is needed; see,
[
KV08
,
Vol11
]
, for instance.

3)

Let us refer to
[
KV12
]
, where POD a-posteriori error estimates are combined with a sequential quadratic programming method in order to solve a nonlinear PDE constrained optimal control problem. Furthermore, the presented analysis for linear-quadratic problems can be extended to semilinear optimal control problems by a second-order analysis; see in
[
KTV13
]
.
\blacksquare

0:

Initial control
u^{\ell 0}\in{\mathscr{U}}
, initial number
\ell
for the POD ansatz functions, a maximal number
\ell_{\mathsf{max}}>\ell
of POD ansatz functions, maximal number
k_{\mathsf{max}}
of iterations and a stopping tolerance
\epsilon>0
.

1:

Set
k=0
and determine
\hat{y}
,
\hat{p}
,
y^{1}=\mathcal{S}u^{\ell 0}
,
y^{2}=\mathcal{A}u^{\ell 0}
.

2:

Compute a POD basis
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell_{\mathsf{max}}}
choosing
y^{1}
and
y^{2}
.

3:

repeat

4:

Establish the POD Galerkin discretization using
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
.

5:

Set
k=k+1
and call Algorithm 4.2 to compute suboptimal control
\bar{u}^{\ell k}
.

6:

Determine
\zeta^{\ell}
according to Theorem
4.2.14
and compute
\epsilon_{\mathsf{ape}}=\|\zeta^{\ell}\|_{\mathscr{U}}/\sigma
.

7:

if

\epsilon_{\mathsf{ape}}<\epsilon

or

\ell=\ell_{\mathsf{max}}

then

8:

Return
\ell
and suboptimal control
\bar{u}^{\ell k}
and STOP.

9:

end

if

10:

Set
\ell=\ell+1
.

11:

until

\ell>\ell_{\mathsf{max}}

or

k=k_{\mathsf{max}}

4.3
The semidiscrete approximation

While the last section dealt with POD approximations of the infinite-dimensional optimal control problem (
P
), in this section we deal with the more practically relevant situation where the state equation undergoes a semidiscretization, replacing the spaces
V
and
H
in the Gelfand triple by a finite-dimensional space
V^{h}
. We then study a POD approximation of the semidiscretized system, which is in practice used to further reduce the computational complexity of the problem.

4.3.1
Galerkin discretization

In Section
3.4.1
we have introduced the
m
-dimensional subspace

\displaystyle V^{h}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\varphi_{1}^{h},\ldots,\varphi_{m}^{h}\big\}\subset V.

Suppose that
\hat{y}:[0,T]\to V^{h}
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle\hat{y}^{h}(t),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\hat{y}^{h}(t),\varphi^{h})

\displaystyle={\langle\mathcal{F}(t),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\quad\text{for all }\varphi^{h}\in V^{h}\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

(4.3.1a)

\displaystyle\hat{y}^{h}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{h}y_{\circ},

(4.3.1b)

where
\mathcal{P}^{h}:X\to V^{h}
is an appropriate linear projection operator and
X
stands either for the space
H
or for the space
V
. In Example
3.4.4
we have presented two possible choices for
\mathcal{P}^{h}
. It follows from Theorem
3.4.5
that there exists a unique solution
\hat{y}^{h}
to (
4.3.1
) satisfying
\hat{y}^{h}\in H^{1}(0,T;V)
and

\displaystyle{\|\hat{y}^{h}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq C\Big({\|\mathcal{P}^{h}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}+{\|\mathcal{F}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}\Big)

for a constant
C>0
which is independent of
y_{\circ}
and
f
. Moreover, we define the linear solution operator
\mathcal{S}^{h}:{\mathscr{U}}\to{\mathscr{Y}}
as follows: for given
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
let
y^{h}=\mathcal{S}^{h}u
be the solution to

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle y^{h}(t),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{H}+a(t;y^{h}(t),\varphi^{h})

\displaystyle={\langle(\mathcal{B}u)(t),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\quad\text{for all }\varphi^{h}\in V^{h}\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

(4.3.2a)

\displaystyle y^{h}(0)

\displaystyle=0.

(4.3.2b)

Due to Theorem
3.4.5
the operator
\mathcal{S}^{h}
is well-defined and bounded. Moreover,
y^{h}=\hat{y}^{h}+\mathcal{S}^{h}u
,
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
, is the unique solution to

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle y^{h}(t),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{H}+a(t;y^{h}(t),\varphi^{h})

\displaystyle={\langle(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{B}u)(t),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\quad\text{for all }\varphi^{h}\in V^{h}\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

(4.3.3a)

\displaystyle y^{h}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{h}y_{\circ},

(4.3.3b)

which has already been introduced in (
3.4.4
). Now, we define the following Galerkin approximation for the reduced cost functional:

\displaystyle\hat{J}^{h}(u)=J(\hat{y}^{h}+\mathcal{S}^{h}u,u)\quad\text{for }u\in{\mathscr{U}}.

The Galerkin approximation for (

^
P

) reads

\min\hat{J}^{h}(u)\quad\text{s.t.}\quad u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

(
\mathbf{\hat{P}}^{h}
)

Problem (

^
P

h

) is a linear-quadratic optimal control problem. Since
\mathcal{S}^{h}
is a linear and bounded operator, existence and uniqueness of a solution
\bar{u}^{h}\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
to (

^
P

h

) follows from Theorem
4.1.14
. Moreover, a first-order sufficient optimality condition is given by

{\langle\sigma(\bar{u}^{h}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p}^{h},u-\bar{u}^{h}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}\geq 0\quad\text{for all }u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}},

(4.3.4)

where
\bar{p}^{h}
solves the adjoint equation

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle\bar{p}^{h}(t),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\varphi^{h},\bar{p}^{h}(t))

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\,{\langle{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)-\bar{y}^{h}(t),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{H}\quad\text{for all }\varphi^{h}\in V^{h}\text{ a.e. in }[0,T),

(4.3.5a)

\displaystyle\bar{p}^{h}(T)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}^{h}{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-\bar{y}^{h}(T)\big)

(4.3.5b)

and
\bar{y}^{h}=\hat{y}^{h}+\mathcal{S}^{h}\bar{u}
holds. In (
4.3.4
) we have utilized that the gradient
\nabla\hat{J}^{h}
of the reduced cost functional
\hat{J}^{h}
is given by the formula

\nabla\hat{J}^{h}(u)=\sigma(u-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\hat{p}^{h}+\mathcal{A}^{h}u)\quad\text{for }u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

(4.3.6)

Note that (
4.3.5
) is the Galerkin scheme for the adjoint equation (
4.1.12
). For
\bar{p}^{h}\in H^{1}(0,T;V^{h})
we have the representation

\bar{p}^{h}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\bar{\mathrm{p}}_{j}^{h}(t)\varphi_{j}^{h}\quad\text{f.a.a. }t\in[0,T]

(4.3.7)

with a coefficient function
\bar{\mathrm{p}}^{h}=(\bar{\mathrm{p}}_{1}^{h},\ldots,\bar{\mathrm{p}}_{m}^{h}):[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Moreover, we introduce the two
m
-dimensional vectors

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}_{1}^{h}(t)=\big({\langle{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t),\varphi^{h}_{i}\rangle}_{H}\big)_{1\leq i\leq m},~t\in[0,T],\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{y}_{2}^{h}=\big(y^{h}_{2i}\big)_{1\leq i\leq m},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{h}{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}=\sum_{i=1}^{h}\mathrm{y}_{2i}^{h}\varphi^{h}_{i}\in V^{h}.

Recall that the matrices
{\bm{M}}^{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
and
{\bm{A}}^{h}(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
have been defined in Example
3.4.4
and (
3.4.14
), respectively. Then inserting (
4.3.7
) into (
4.3.5a
) and choosing
\varphi^{h}=\varphi^{h}_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,m
, we derive the following end-value problem

\displaystyle-{\bm{M}}^{h}\dot{\bar{\mathrm{p}}}^{h}(t)+{\bm{A}}^{h,\top}(t)\bar{\mathrm{p}}^{h}(t)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\big(\mathrm{y}_{1}^{h}(t)-{\bm{M}}^{h}\bar{\mathrm{y}}^{h}(t)\big)

\displaystyle\text{for }t\in(0,T],

(4.3.8)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathrm{p}}^{h}(T)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\big(\mathrm{y}_{2}^{h}-{\bm{M}}^{h}\bar{\mathrm{y}}^{h}(T)\big),

where
\bar{\mathrm{y}}^{h}:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is the solution to (
3.4.13
) for the choice
u=\bar{u}^{h}
.

Let
\hat{p}^{h}\in H^{1}(0,T;V^{h})
be the unique solution to

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle\hat{p}^{h}(t),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\varphi^{h},\hat{p}^{h}(t))

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\,{\langle{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)-\hat{y}^{h}(t),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{H}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in V^{h}\text{ a.e. in }[0,T),

(4.3.9a)

\displaystyle\hat{p}^{h}(T)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}^{h}{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-\hat{y}^{h}(T)\big).

(4.3.9b)

Moreover, we introduce the linear and bounded operator
\mathcal{A}^{h}:{\mathscr{U}}\to{\mathscr{Y}}
as follows: for given
u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
the function
p^{h}=\mathcal{A}^{h}u
is the unique solution to

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle p^{h}(t),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\varphi^{h},p^{h}(t))

\displaystyle=-\sigma_{1}\,{\langle(\mathcal{S}^{h}u)(t),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{H}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in V^{h}\text{ a.e. in }[0,T),

(4.3.10a)

\displaystyle p^{h}(T)

\displaystyle=-\sigma_{2}(\mathcal{S}^{h}u)(T).

(4.3.10b)

Then the solution to (
4.3.5
) can be expressed as
\bar{p}=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}^{h}\bar{u}
.

Example 4.3.1
.

Now we introduce an example for an optimal control problem, which will be used for the numerical tests throughout this section. For the PDE we take our guiding model, which was described in Example
3.5.10
together with the initial state
y_{\circ}({\bm{x}})=17
. The reduced cost function
\hat{J}^{h}:{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}\to\mathbb{R}
is given by

\displaystyle\hat{J}^{h}(u)=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{1}}{2}\int_{0}^{T}{\|(S^{h}u)(t)+\hat{y}^{h}(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

(4.3.11)

\displaystyle+\frac{\sigma_{2}}{2}\,{\|(S^{h}u)(T)+\hat{y}^{h}(T)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{\sigma}{2}{\|u-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2},

where we set
\sigma_{1}=1
,
\sigma_{2}=0.1
and
\sigma=0.001
. Moreover, we choose the desired states
{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t):=17
for all
t\in(0,T)
and
{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}:=17
, as well as the desired control
{u^{\mathsf{n}}}(t)=0
for all
t\in(0,T)
. In other words the goal of our optimal control problem is to maintain the constant initial temperature of
17^{\circ}
in the entire room in spite of the heat exchange with the outer world and the advection in the room.
\blacklozenge

4.3.2
POD basis computation

Suppose that Assumptions
4.1.1
and
4.1.8
hold. Let
u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
be given,
y^{h}=\hat{y}^{h}+\mathcal{S}^{h}u
and
p^{h}=\hat{p}^{h}+\mathcal{A}^{h}u
. Then we have
y^{h},p^{h}\in H^{1}(0,T;V)
; cf. Assumption
4.2.2
-1). In the context of Section
2.4.1
we choose
K=2
,
\omega_{1}^{K}=\omega_{2}^{K}=1
,
y^{1}=y^{h}
and
y^{2}=p^{h}
so that the
snapshot space
is given by

\mathscr{V}=\mathrm{span}\,\bigg\{\int_{0}^{T}\phi_{1}(t)y^{h}(t)+\phi_{2}(t)p^{h}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\,\Big|\,\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in L^{2}(0,T)\bigg\}\subset V,

(4.3.12)

with finite dimension
d=\dim\mathscr{V}\leq m
. Therefore, it follows from
[
Sin14
, Lemma 3.1-2)]
that
d
is the same for the choices
X=H
and
X=V
. Suppose that
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset V^{h}
is a POD basis of rank
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
solving

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\min\int_{0}^{T}\Big\|y^{h}(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y^{h}(t),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{X}^{2}+\Big\|p^{h}(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle p^{h}(t),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{X}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 2.84526pt\text{s.t. }\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset X\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle}_{X}=\delta_{ij}\quad\text{ for }1\leq i,j\leq\ell.\end{aligned}\right.

(4.3.13)

Recall that the solution to (
4.3.13
) is given by the solution to the eigenvalue problem (
3.4.18
), where the operator
\mathcal{R}:X\to V^{h}\subset X
is given as

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\psi=\int_{0}^{T}\Big({\langle y^{h}(t),\psi\rangle}_{X}\,y^{h}(t)+{\langle p^{h}(t),\psi\rangle}_{X}\,p^{h}(t)\Big)\,\mathrm{d}t\quad\text{for }\psi\in X

In particular,
\lambda_{i}=0
for all
i>d
; cf. Assumption
4.2.2
-3). Then we obtain

\int_{0}^{T}\Big\|y^{h}(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle y^{h}(t),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{X}^{2}+\Big\|p^{h}(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle p^{h}(t),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\psi_{i}\Big\|_{X}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t=\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d}\lambda_{i}.

(4.3.14)

We set

\displaystyle X^{\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}\big\}\subset V\subset X

and

\displaystyle H^{\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\psi_{1}^{H},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}^{H}\big\},\quad V^{\ell}=\mathrm{span}\,\big\{\psi_{1}^{V},\ldots,\psi_{\ell}^{V}\big\}

for the choices
X=H
and
X=V
, respectively. For the reader’s convenience we omitted the dependence of the eigenvectors
\{\psi_{i}^{H}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
and
\{\psi_{i}^{V}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
on the spatial discretization by an index
h
. Finally, we replace Assumption
4.2.2
by the following one.

Assumption 4.3.2
.

1)

Let Assumptions
4.1.1
and
4.1.8
hold.

2)

The Hilbert space
X
denotes either
H
or
V
. In case of
X=V
we suppose that
y_{\circ}\in V
is valid.

3)

We set
K=2
,
\omega_{1}^{K}=\omega_{2}^{K}=1
and
y^{1}=y^{h}
,
y^{2}=p^{h}
. The snapshot space
\mathscr{V}
is given by (
4.3.12
) with finite dimension
d\leq m
. Let
\{\psi_{i}^{H}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
and
\{\psi_{i}^{V}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
be solutions to (

P
ℓ

H

) and (

P
ℓ

V

), respectively.

4)

In case of
X=H
, let
\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}
denote
\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}_{H}
. If
X=V
, let it be given by
\mathcal{Q}^{h\ell}_{H}
.

4.3.3
POD Galerkin scheme for the dual equation

Suppose that Assumption
4.3.2
hold. In Section
3.4.4
we have introduced a POD Galerkin scheme for the state equation. From Theorem
3.4.8
and
\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y_{\circ}\|_{H}\leq\|y_{\circ}\|_{H}
we infer that

{\|y^{h\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq C\left({\|y_{\circ}\|}_{H}+{\|\mathcal{F}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}+{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\right)

(4.3.15)

for a constant
C>0
which is independent of
y_{\circ}
,
\mathcal{F}
,
u
and
\ell
. Due to (
3.4.27
) there exists a constant
C>0
satisfying the a-priori error estimate

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}{\|y^{h}(t)-y^{h\ell}(t)\|}_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\cdot\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{H}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{H}\big\|_{V}^{2}&&\text{for }X=H,\\
&\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{V}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{V}-\mathcal{Q}_{H}^{h\ell}\psi_{i}^{V}\big\|_{V}^{2}&&\text{for }X=V,\end{aligned}\right.

where
y^{h}=\hat{y}^{h}+\mathcal{S}^{h}u
and
y^{h\ell}=\hat{y}^{h\ell}+\mathcal{S}^{h\ell}u
denote the solutions to (
3.4.4
) and (
3.4.23
), respectively.

Analogously, we proceed for the dual equation. Making the ansatz

p^{h\ell}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{p}_{i}^{h\ell}(t)\psi_{i}^{h}\in X^{h\ell},\quad t\in[0,T]

(4.3.16)

and plugging this into (
4.3.5
) leads to the following POD Galerkin scheme:

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle p^{h\ell}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\psi,p^{h\ell}(t))

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\,{\langle{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)-y^{h\ell}(t),\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\text{ for all }\psi\in X^{\ell}\text{ a.e. in }[0,T),

(4.3.17a)

\displaystyle\hat{p}^{h\ell}(T)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-y^{h\ell}(T)\big)

(4.3.17b)

with
y^{h\ell}=\hat{y}^{h\ell}+\mathcal{S}^{h\ell}u
. Let us also define the
\ell
-dimensional vectors

\displaystyle\mathrm{p}^{h\ell}(t)=\big(\mathrm{p}_{i}^{h\ell}(t)\big)_{1\leq i\leq\ell},\quad\mathrm{y}_{1}^{h\ell}=\big({\langle{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t),\psi_{i}\rangle}_{H}\big)_{1\leq i\leq\ell},\quad\mathrm{y}_{2}^{h\ell}=\big(\mathrm{y}_{2i}^{h\ell}\big)_{1\leq i\leq\ell},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{y}_{2i}^{h\ell}\psi_{i}\in X^{h\ell}.

In Section
3.4.4
we have introduced the
(\ell\times\ell)
-matrices
\mathrm{M}^{h\ell}
and
\mathrm{A}^{h\ell}(t)
,
t\in[0,T]
. Then (
4.3.17
) can be expressed as the following system of ordinary differential equations

\displaystyle-{\bm{M}}^{h\ell}\dot{\mathrm{p}}^{h\ell}(t)+{\bm{A}}^{h\ell}(t)^{\top}\mathrm{p}^{h\ell}(t)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\big(\mathrm{y}_{1}^{h\ell}-{\bm{M}}^{h\ell}\mathrm{y}^{h\ell}(t)\big),\quad t\in[0,T),

(4.3.18)

\displaystyle\mathrm{p}^{h\ell}(T)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\big(\mathrm{y}_{2}^{h\ell}-\mathrm{y}^{h\ell}(T)\big),

where the vector
\mathrm{y}^{h\ell}(t)
,
t\in[0,T]
, solves (
3.4.25
). Note that
\tilde{p}^{h\ell}(t)=p^{h\ell}(T-t)
,
t\in[0,T]
, satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle\tilde{p}^{h\ell}(t),\psi^{h}\rangle}_{H}+\tilde{a}(t;\psi^{h},\tilde{p}^{h\ell}(t))

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\,{\langle{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)-y^{h\ell}(T-t),\psi^{h}\rangle}_{H}\text{ for all }\psi^{h}\in X^{\ell}\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

(4.3.19a)

\displaystyle\tilde{p}^{h\ell}(T)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-y^{h\ell}(T)\big).

(4.3.19b)

Now, existence and uniqueness of a solution
\tilde{p}
to (
4.3.19
) can be shown by similar arguments as in the proof of Theorem
3.4.8
. Thus, (
4.3.17
) is also uniquely solvable and
p^{h\ell}(t)=\tilde{p}^{h\ell}(T-t)
,
t\in[0,T]
, is the unique solution. Summarizing, we obtain the next result.

Proposition 4.3.3
.

Let Assumption
4.3.2
hold. Then for every
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
there exists a unique
p^{h\ell}\in H^{1}(0,T;V)\hookrightarrow{\mathscr{Y}}
solving (
4.3.17
) and satisfying

\displaystyle{\|p^{h\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq C\big(\sigma_{2}\,{\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-y^{h\ell}(T)\|}_{H}+\sigma_{1}\,{\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}-y^{h\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}\big)

for a constant
C\geq 0
which is independent of
\ell
and
y^{h\ell}=\hat{y}^{h\ell}+\mathcal{S}^{h\ell}u
.

The following corollary follows by analogous arguments utilized in the proof of Corollary
4.2.4
and in Remark
4.2.5
-1).

Corollary 4.3.4
.

Let Assumption
4.3.2
hold.

1)

There exists a unique solution
\hat{p}^{h\ell}\in H^{1}(0,T;V)\hookrightarrow{\mathscr{Y}}
to

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle\hat{p}^{h\ell}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\psi,\hat{p}^{h\ell}(t))

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\,{\langle{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)-\hat{y}(t),\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\text{ for all }\psi\in X^{\ell}\text{ a.e. in }[0,T),

(4.3.20)

\displaystyle\hat{p}^{h\ell}(T)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\mathcal{P}^{\ell}{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-\hat{y}^{h\ell}(T).

In particular, we have

\displaystyle{\|\hat{p}^{h\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq C\big(\sigma_{2}\,{\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-\hat{y}^{h\ell}(T)\|}_{H}+\sigma_{1}\,{\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}-\hat{y}^{h\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}\big)

for a constant
C\geq 0
which is independent of
\ell
.

2)

Let us introduce the linear operator
\mathcal{A}^{h\ell}:{\mathscr{U}}\to{\mathscr{Y}}
as follows:
p^{h\ell}=\mathcal{A}^{h\ell}u
,
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
, satisfies

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle p^{h\ell}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\psi,p^{h\ell}(t))

\displaystyle=-\sigma_{1}\,{\langle(\mathcal{S}^{h\ell}u)(t),\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\quad\text{for all }\psi\in X^{\ell}\text{ a.e. in }[0,T),

\displaystyle p^{h\ell}(T)

\displaystyle=-\sigma_{2}(\mathcal{S}^{h\ell}u)(T).

Then
\mathcal{A}^{h\ell}
is well-defined and bounded. In particular,

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{A}^{h\ell}u\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq C\left(\sigma_{2}\,{\|(\mathcal{S}^{h\ell}u)(T)\|}_{H}+\sigma_{1}\,{\|\mathcal{S}^{h\ell}u\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}\right)

for a constant
C\geq 0
which is independent of
\ell
.

3)

The solution to (
4.3.17
) can be expressed as
p^{h\ell}=\hat{p}^{h\ell}+\mathcal{A}^{h\ell}u
.

The next result is proved in Section
4.5.3
.

Corollary 4.3.5
.

Let all assumptions of Proposition
4.3.3
hold. Then

\displaystyle{\|p^{h\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq C\,\big({\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}+{\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}+{\|\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}+{\|\mathcal{F}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}+{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\big)

for a constant
C\geq 0
which is independent of
\ell
.

4.3.4
POD a-priori error analysis for the dual equation

We suppose Assumption
4.3.2
and proceed similarly to Section
4.2.2
. Then
\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}
is an
H
-orthonormal projection for
X=H
and
X=V
, respectively. Our next goal is to derive a-priori error estimates for the term

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}{\|p^{h}(t)-p^{h\ell}(t)\|}_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t,

where
p^{h}
and
p^{h\ell}
are the solutions to (
4.3.5
) and (
4.3.17
), respectively. Following the arguments of the proof of Theorem
4.2.8
we derive the following result.

Theorem 4.3.6
.

Assume that Assumption
4.3.2
hold. Furthermore,
p^{\ell}
and
p^{h\ell}
denote the unique solutions to (
4.3.5
) and (
4.3.17
), respectively. Then there exists a constant
C>0
satisfying the a-priori error estimate

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\big\|p^{h}(t)-p^{h\ell}(t)\big\|_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\cdot\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{H}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{H}\big\|_{V}^{2}&&\text{for }X=H,\\
&\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{V}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{V}-\mathcal{Q}_{H}^{h\ell}\psi_{i}^{V}\big\|_{V}^{2}&&\text{for }X=V.\end{aligned}\right.

Example 4.3.7
.

Let us now verify the statement of Theorem
4.3.6
numerically. To do so we use the guiding example for this section introduced in Example
4.3.1
. For a random control
u
we solve the associated state and dual equations (
4.3.3
) and (
4.3.5
), respectively, to obtain the state
y^{h}(u)
and the dual state
p^{h}(u)
. Following the procedure presented in Section
4.3.2
a POD basis
\left\{\Psi_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset V^{h}
is computed using the snapshots
y^{h}(u)
and
p^{h}(u)
. For this POD basis we can then compute the solution
p^{h\ell}(u)
of the POD dual equation (
4.3.17
).

Figure 4.3.1
:
Example
4.3.7
. Plot of the a-priori error estimate and the approximation error of the dual states for different choices of spaces and projections (y-axis: logarithmic scale).

In Figure
4.3.1
the error between
p^{h}(u)
and
p^{h\ell}(u)
is depicted for a varying number of POD basis elements. As expected the error decays when increasing the number of basis elements. At the same time it can be observed that the a-priori error estimates for both choices of the space and the projection predict the qualitative behaviour of the error nicely. Let us note again that we plot the a-priori estimates with the constant
C=1
, since we are only interested in obtaining a qualitative but not a quantitative estimate of the error.
\blacklozenge

4.3.5
POD Galerkin scheme for the optimality system

The POD Galerkin approximation for (

^
P

h

) is as follows:

\min\hat{J}^{h\ell}(u)\quad\text{s.t.}\quad u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}},

(
\mathbf{\hat{P}}^{h\ell}
)

where the objective is defined as
\hat{J}^{h\ell}(u)=J(\hat{y}^{h\ell}+\mathcal{S}^{h\ell}u,u)
for
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
. Note that (

^
P

⁢
h
ℓ

) is a convex quadratic programming problem. An optimal solution
\bar{u}^{h\ell}\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
to (

^
P

⁢
h
ℓ

) is characterized by the first-order sufficient optimality condition

{\langle\sigma(\bar{u}^{h\ell}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p}^{h\ell},u-\bar{u}^{h\ell}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}\geq 0\quad\text{for all }u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}

(4.3.21)

with
\bar{p}^{h\ell}=\hat{p}^{h\ell}+\mathcal{A}^{h\ell}\bar{u}^{h\ell}
. In (
4.3.21
) we have utilized that the gradient
\nabla\hat{J}^{h\ell}
of the reduced cost functional
\hat{J}^{h\ell}
is given by the formula

\nabla\hat{J}^{h\ell}(u)=\sigma(u-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\hat{p}^{h\ell}+\mathcal{A}^{h\ell}u)\quad\text{for }u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

(4.3.22)

Since Theorem
4.2.12
can be extended easily to the semidiscrete case, we derive the following result.

Theorem 4.3.8
.

Assume that Assumption
4.3.2
hold. Let
\bar{u}^{h}\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
be the optimal solution to (
4.2.12
). Moreover,
\bar{u}^{h\ell}
denotes the unique solution to (
4.3.21
). Then there is a constant
C>0
satisfying the a-priori error estimate

\big\|\bar{u}^{h}-\bar{u}^{h\ell}\big\|_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}\leq C\cdot\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{H}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{H}\big\|_{V}^{2}&&\text{for }X=H,\\
&\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{V}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{V}-\mathcal{Q}_{H}^{h\ell}\psi_{i}^{V}\big\|_{V}^{2}&&\text{for }X=V,\end{aligned}\right.

(4.3.23)

where the constant
C
depends on
c_{V}
,
\gamma
,
\gamma_{1}
,
T
,
\sigma_{1}
,
\sigma_{2}
and
\|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(0,T;V),{\mathscr{U}})}
.

Example 4.3.9
.

For the optimal control problem from Example
4.3.1
we investigate the statement of Theorem
4.3.8
numerically. Therefore, we compute the solution
\bar{u}^{h}
to (
4.2.12
). For this control we compute the associated state
y^{h}(\bar{u})
and dual state
p^{h}(\bar{u})
by solving the state equation (
4.3.3
) and the dual state equation (
4.3.5
), respectively, and use these as snapshots for the computation of a POD basis. In Figure
4.3.2
the error between
u^{h}
and
u^{h\ell}
together with the a-priori error estimates for the two choices of the space and the projection can be seen.

Figure 4.3.2
:
Example
4.3.9
. Plot of the a-priori error estimate and the approximation error
\|\bar{u}^{h}-\bar{u}^{h\ell}\|_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}
between the optimal controls for different choices of spaces and projections (y-axis: logarithmic scale).

In both cases the a-priori estimates describe the qualitative behaviour of the error quite well up to a basis size of around 50. When further enlarging the POD basis a stagnation of the error in the controls can be observed, which can be explained with the predefined accuracy with which we solve the two optimal control problems. Hence, for the given stopping tolerances for the optimal control problems it is not meaningful to use more than 50 basis functions, since the error in the controls already reaches the computational accuracy at this point. To underline the importance of a good choice of snapshots for the POD basis computation, we conduct the same experiment again with two different choices of snapshots. First, we take the snapshots
y^{h}(u)
and
p^{h}(u)
which are associated to a randomly chosen control
u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
. Secondly, we use the snapshots
y^{h}(\tilde{u})
and
p^{h}(\tilde{u})
, where
\tilde{u}\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is a suboptimal control, which is obtained by only computing a few iterations in the optimization process. The results of this experiment can be seen in Figure
4.3.3
.

Figure 4.3.3
:
Example
4.3.9
. Plot of the approximation error
\|\bar{u}^{h}-\bar{u}^{h\ell}\|_{\mathscr{U}}
between the optimal controls for different choices of snapshots (y-axis: logarithmic scale).

As expected the snapshots taken from a randomly chosen control do not contain the dynamics close to the optimal control, so that the approximation error is large even for a large number of POD basis functions. Taking a suboptimal control for generating the snapshots yields already better results. In fact, for a basis size of up to 20 the approximation error is of the same order as for the optimal snapshots. Only when further increasing the number of basis function we can see a clear difference between the optimal and the suboptimal snapshots.
\blacklozenge

4.3.6
POD a-posteriori error analysis for the reduced gradient

Now we want to control the error in the gradient of the reduced cost functional by an a-posteriori error estiomate. Let
u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
be chosen arbitrarily. Then we have from (
4.3.6
) and (
4.3.22
) that

\nabla J^{h}(u)-\nabla\hat{J}^{h\ell}(u)=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\big(p^{h\ell}(u)-p^{h}(u)\big)

(4.3.24)

with the dual variables
p^{h}(u)=\hat{p}^{h}+\mathcal{A}^{h}u
and
p^{h\ell}(u)=\hat{p}^{h\ell}+\mathcal{A}^{h\ell}u
. Thus, an a-posteriori error estimate for the difference
\nabla J^{h}(u)-\nabla\hat{J}^{h\ell}(u)
is based on the availability of an a-posteriori error estimate for the difference
p^{h\ell}(u)-p^{h}(u)
. This is stated in the next proposition.

Proposition 4.3.10
.

Suppose that Assumptions
3.2.1
and
4.1.8
hold. For an arbitrarily given
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
let
p^{h}(u)=\hat{p}^{h}+\mathcal{A}^{h}u
and
p^{h\ell}(u)=\hat{p}^{h\ell}+\mathcal{A}^{h\ell}u
. Then the a-posteriori error estimate

\displaystyle{\|p^{h}(t;u)-p^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{R}_{T}^{h\ell}+\mathsf{C}_{2}^{h\ell}\bigg(\mathsf{R}_{\circ}^{h\ell}+\int_{0}^{T}\mathsf{R}_{1}^{h\ell}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\bigg)+\frac{2}{\gamma_{1}}\int_{t}^{T}{\|\mathsf{r}^{h\ell}(s;u)\|}_{(V^{h})^{\prime}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

(4.3.25)

\displaystyle+\int_{t}^{T}\mathsf{C}_{3}^{h\ell}(s)\bigg(\mathsf{R}_{\circ}^{h\ell}+\int_{0}^{s}\mathsf{R}_{1}^{h\ell}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau\bigg)\,\mathrm{d}s

is satisfied for almost all
t\in[0,T]
, where

\mathsf{R}_{T}^{h\ell}=2\sigma_{2}\,{\|(\mathcal{P}^{h}-\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}){y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}^{2},~\mathsf{C}_{2}^{h\ell}=2\sigma_{2}\,\mathsf{C}_{1}^{h\ell}(T),~\mathsf{C}_{3}^{h\ell}(s)=\frac{2\sigma_{1}^{2}c_{V}^{2}}{\gamma_{1}}\,\mathsf{C}_{1}^{h\ell}(s)

(4.3.26)

holds, the (dual) residual
\mathsf{r}^{h\ell}(t)\in(V^{h})^{\prime}
,
t\in[0,T]
a.e., is defined as

\mathsf{r}^{h\ell}(t)=-{\langle p_{t}^{h\ell}(t;u),\cdot\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}+a(t;\cdot\,,p^{h\ell}(t;u))-\sigma_{1}\,{\langle{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)-y^{h\ell}(t;u)\rangle}_{H}

(4.3.27)

and the constants
\mathsf{C}_{1}^{h\ell}(t)
,
\mathsf{R}_{\circ}^{h\ell}
and
\mathsf{R}_{1}^{h\ell}(s)
are given by (
3.6.19a
). Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}{\|p^{h}(t;u)-p^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

(4.3.28)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\,\bigg(\mathsf{R}_{T}^{h\ell}+\mathsf{C}_{2}^{h\ell}\Big(\mathsf{R}_{\circ}^{h\ell}+\int_{0}^{T}\mathsf{R}_{1}^{h\ell}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\Big)\bigg)+\frac{2}{\gamma_{1}}\,{\|\mathsf{r}^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{(V^{h})^{\prime}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\int_{0}^{T}\mathsf{C}_{3}^{h\ell}(s)\bigg(\mathsf{R}_{\circ}^{h\ell}+\int_{0}^{s}\mathsf{R}_{1}^{h\ell}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau\bigg).

From (
4.3.24
) and (
4.3.28
) we derive the following estimate for the gradients of the reduced cost functionals for a given control
u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
:

{\|\nabla J^{h}(u)-\nabla\hat{J}^{h\ell}(u)\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\leq{\|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\|}_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(0,T;V),{\mathscr{U}})}{\|p^{h\ell}(u)-p^{h}(u)\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}\leq\Delta_{\mathsf{apo}}^{h\ell}(u)

(4.3.29)

with the a-posteriori error bound

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathsf{apo}}^{h\ell}(u)

\displaystyle={\|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\|}_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(0,T;V),{\mathscr{U}})}\Bigg(\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\,\bigg(\mathsf{R}_{T}^{h\ell}+\mathsf{C}_{2}^{h\ell}\Big(\mathsf{R}_{\circ}^{h\ell}+\int_{0}^{T}\mathsf{R}_{1}^{h\ell}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\Big)\bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 102.42992pt+\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\int_{0}^{T}\mathsf{C}_{3}^{h\ell}(s)\bigg(\mathsf{R}_{\circ}^{h\ell}+\int_{0}^{s}\mathsf{R}_{1}^{h\ell}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau\bigg)+\frac{2}{\gamma_{1}}\,{\|\mathsf{r}^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{(V^{h})^{\prime}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\Bigg)^{1/2}.

Estimate (
4.3.29
) is very useful in inexact optimization methods. It can be utilized to control the inexactness of the gradient of reduced-order cost functional. We refer, e.g., to trust-region POD methods, where a so-called
Carter condition
is utilized to ensure global convergence of the iterative trust-region method; cf.
[
AFS00
,
RTV17
,
Sch13
]
.

4.3.7
POD a-posteriori error analysis for the optimality system

Proceeding as in Section
4.2.4
we can derive an a-posteriori error estimate for the difference
\|\bar{u}^{h}-\bar{u}^{h\ell}\|_{\mathscr{U}}
, where
\bar{u}^{h}
and
\bar{u}^{h\ell}
are the solutions to (

^
P

h

) and (

^
P

⁢
h
ℓ

), respectively.

Theorem 4.3.11
.

Suppose that Assumptions
3.2.1
and
4.1.8
hold. Let
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
be arbitrarily given so that
\mathcal{S}^{h\ell}u\neq 0
and
\mathcal{A}^{h\ell}u\neq 0
. To compute a POD basis
\{\psi_{i}^{h}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell
we choose
K=2
,
y^{1}=\hat{y}^{h\ell}+\mathcal{S}^{h\ell}u
and
y^{2}=\hat{p}^{h\ell}+\mathcal{A}^{h\ell}u
. Define the function
\zeta^{h\ell}\in{\mathscr{U}}
by

\displaystyle\zeta_{i}^{h\ell}(t)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &-\min(0,\xi_{i}^{h\ell}(s))&&\text{a.e. in }\mathscr{A}^{h\ell}_{\mathsf{a}i}=\big\{s\in\mathscr{D}\,|\bar{u}^{h\ell}_{i}(s)=u_{\mathsf{a}i}(s)\big\},\\
&-\max(0,\xi^{h\ell}_{i}(s))&&\text{a.e. in }\mathscr{A}^{h\ell}_{\mathsf{b}i}=\big\{s\in\mathscr{D}\,|\bar{u}^{h\ell}_{i}(s)=u_{\mathsf{b}i}(s)\big\},\\
&-\xi_{i}^{h\ell}(t)&&\text{a.e. in }\mathscr{D}\setminus\big(\mathscr{A}^{h\ell}_{\mathsf{a}i}\cup\mathscr{A}_{\mathsf{b}i}^{h\ell}\big)\end{aligned}\right.

for
i=1,\ldots,\mathsf{m}
, where
\xi^{h\ell}=\sigma(\bar{u}^{h\ell}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\hat{p}^{h\ell}+\mathcal{A}\bar{u}^{h\ell})
in
{\mathscr{U}}
. Then the a-posteriori error estimate

{\|\bar{u}^{h}-\bar{u}^{h\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\leq\frac{1}{\sigma}\,{\|\zeta^{h\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}

(4.3.30)

holds. In particular,
\lim\limits_{\ell\to\infty}\|\zeta^{h\ell}\|_{\mathscr{U}}=0
.

Remark 4.3.12
.

From a-priori error analysis it is known that

\displaystyle{\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{h}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\leq Ch^{\alpha}

where
\bar{u}
and
\bar{u}^{h}
are the solutions to (

^
P

) and (

^
P

h

), respectively. Then we find

\displaystyle{\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{h\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\leq{\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{h}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}+{\|\bar{u}^{h}-\bar{u}^{h\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}

which is studied in
[
GNV17
]
, for instance.
\Diamond

Example 4.3.13
.

Now we want to verify the a-posteriori error estimate from Theorem
4.3.11
numerically. Again all parameters of the optimal control problem are taken from Example
4.3.1
. As we have seen in Figure
4.3.3
the choice of good snapshots is essential for the approximation quality. Therefore, we take a framework that might very well appear in practice: Given a suboptimal control
\tilde{u}\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
of the optimal control problem, we compute the state
y^{h}(\tilde{u})
as well as the dual state
p^{h}(\tilde{u})
, which are then taken as snapshots for computing the POD basis. With this basis we solve (

^
P

⁢
h
ℓ

) and then compare the error
\|\bar{u}^{h}-\bar{u}^{h\ell}\|_{\mathscr{U}}
with the a-posteriori error estimate from Theorem
4.3.11
. The results of this test can be seen in Figure
4.3.4
.

Figure 4.3.4
:
Example
4.3.13
. Left: Plot of the a-posteriori error estimate and the approximation error
\|\bar{u}^{h}-\bar{u}^{h\ell}\|_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}
between the optimal controls(y-axis: logarithmic scale). Right: Efficiency of the a-posteriori error estimate.

Most importantly we observe that the a-posteriori error estimate is a rigorous error bound, as was shown in Theorem
4.3.11
. The second crucial issue of an a-posteriori error estimate is its efficiency, i.e., the factor by which the estimate overestimates the true error. The reason for this is that one might want to apply the a-posteriori error estimate in an iterative POD basis update scheme as for example shown in Algorithm
4.2.4
. In this case it is important that the overestimation is moderate in order to avoid unnecessary basis updates. For this test case the a-posteriori error estimate turns out to be quite efficient, with the efficiency being between 2 and 20. In general, it can be seen that the efficiency gets worse if the parameter
\sigma
in front of the control cost term
\|u^{h}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\|_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}
is decreased, see Figure
4.3.5
.

Figure 4.3.5
:
Example
4.3.13
. Efficiency of the a-posteriori error estimate for different values of
\sigma
(y-axis: logarithmic scale)

The reason for this lies in the proof of the a-posteriori estimate (cf. the lines before Theorem
4.2.14
). There we made the estimate

\displaystyle 0\leq-\sigma{\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\|}^{2}_{\mathscr{U}}-{\langle\Theta(\bar{y}-\tilde{y}^{\ell}),\bar{y}-\tilde{y}^{\ell}\rangle}_{W_{0}(0,T)^{\prime},W_{0}(0,T)}+{\langle\zeta^{\ell},\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}\leq-\sigma{\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\|}^{2}_{\mathscr{U}}+{\langle\zeta^{\ell},\bar{u}^{\ell}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}.

In our case it holds

\displaystyle{\langle\Theta(\bar{y}-\tilde{y}^{\ell}),\bar{y}-\tilde{y}^{\ell}\rangle}_{W_{0}(0,T)^{\prime},W_{0}(0,T)}=\sigma_{1}\int_{0}^{T}{\|y^{h}(\bar{u})(t)-y^{h}(\bar{u}^{\ell})(t)\|}_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{2}{\|y^{h}(\bar{u})(T)-y^{h}(\bar{u}^{\ell})(T)\|}_{H}^{2},

which is independent of
\sigma
. So especially when decreasing
\sigma
while keeping
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
constant using the estimate
{\langle\Theta(\bar{y}-\tilde{y}^{\ell}),\bar{y}-\tilde{y}^{\ell}\rangle}\geq 0
yields a large overestimation of the approximation error between the controls. Note that there are two values for the basis length
\ell
, for which the efficiency of the a-posteriori error estimate is smaller than one, i.e., it is no upper bound of the error. This is due to the fact that we cannot solve the optimal control problem (

^
P

h

) exactly, but only with respect to some tolerance. However, the a-posteriori estimate gives an upper bound of the error with respect to the exact optimal control of (

^
P

h

). Therefore, it might happen that the error between
u^{h\ell}
and the computed (inexact) optimal control
\bar{u}^{h}
of (

^
P

h

) is larger than the a-posteriori error estimate.
\blacklozenge

4.4
Optimality system POD

The accuracy of the reduced-order model can be controlled by the a-posteriori error analysis presented, e.g., in Section
4.2.3
. However, if the POD basis is created from a reference trajectory containing features which are quite different from those of the optimally controlled trajectory, a rather huge number of POD basis functions has to be included in the reduced-order model. This fact may lead to non-efficient reduced-order models and numerical instabilities. To avoid these problems the POD basis is generated utilizing
optimality system POD
introduced in
[
KV08
]
and extended in
[
Gri13
,
GGV15
,
Gub16
,
Vol11
]
.

Let us consider

\min\hat{J}^{\ell}(u)\quad\text{s.t.}\quad u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}},

(
\mathbf{\hat{P}}^{\ell}
)

where
\hat{J}^{\ell}(u)=J(\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u)
holds and
j
has been introduced in (
4.1.4
). Suppose that Assumption
4.2.2
is satisfied and that for an appropriately chosen
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
the POD basis
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset V
solves the eigenvalue problem

\mathcal{R}\psi_{i}=\lambda_{i}\psi_{i}\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,\ell,\quad\lambda_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{\ell}>0

(4.4.1)

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\psi=\int_{0}^{T}{\langle y(t),\psi\rangle}_{X}\,y(t)+{\langle p(t),\psi\rangle}_{X}\,p(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\quad\text{for }\psi\in X,

where
y=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u
and
p=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}u
solve (
4.1.2
) and (
4.1.12
), respectively. Thus, the POD basis
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
depends on
y
,
p
and thus on the control
u
. This may deter from one of the main advantages of the approach for model reduction which consists in the fact that unlike typical finite element basis functions the elements of the basis functions reflect the dynamics of the system. In optimal control this feature gets lost if the dynamics of the state and the adjoint corresponding to the reference control are significantly different from that of the trajectories corresponding to the optimal control solving

\min\hat{J}(u)\quad\text{s.t.}\quad u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

(
\mathbf{\hat{P}}
)

For the latter case we have the a-priori error analysis presented in Theorem
4.2.12
. To eliminate this weakness of the conventional approach we propose to consider the following enlarged minimization problem

\displaystyle\min J(y^{\ell}_{[u]},u)

(
\mathbf{P}^{\ell}_{\mathsf{os}}
)

\displaystyle\text{s.t. }y^{\ell}_{[u]}=\hat{y}^{\ell}+\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u,~u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}},

\displaystyle\text{\phantom{s.t. }}y_{[u]}=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u,~p_{[u]}=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}u,

\displaystyle\text{\phantom{s.t. }}\mathcal{R}_{[u]}\psi_{i[u]}=\lambda_{i[u]}\psi_{i[u]}\text{ and }{\langle\psi_{i[u]},\psi_{j[u]}\rangle}_{X}=\delta_{ij}\text{ for }i,j=1,\ldots,\ell.

In (

P
ℓ

os

) we indicate by the subindex
[u]
that the POD basis and that all reduced quantities depends on the control
u
. The first three lines coincide with the conventional POD problem (

^
P

ℓ

). The next line stands for the non-reduced state and adjoint equations and the last line represents the eigenvalue problem characterizing the POD basis associated with the current control variable
u
. The optimal solution
(\bar{y}^{\ell},\bar{y},\bar{p},\bar{\psi},\bar{\lambda},\bar{u})
to (

P
ℓ

os

) has the property that the associated POD reduced system is computed from the trajectory corresponding to the optimal control
\bar{u}
and thus, differently from (

^
P

ℓ

), the problem of unmodelled dynamics is removed. Of course, (

P
ℓ

os

) is more complicated than the original problem (

^
P

) and we thus need to justify our approach.

Next we formulate (

P
ℓ

os

) as a constrained optimization problem. Recall that the
state space
is given as
{\mathscr{Y}}=W(0,T)
. We set

\displaystyle\mathbb{X}^{\ell}=\underbrace{X\times\ldots\times X}_{\ell\text{-times}}.

Let us introduce the solution space for the minimization variables as

\displaystyle{\mathscr{X}}=H^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{\ell})\times{\mathscr{Y}}\times{\mathscr{Y}}\times\mathbb{X}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\times{\mathscr{U}}

which is a product Hilbert space endowed with the common product topology. Next we turn to the equality constraints. Utilizing (
3.3.7
) we express the relationship
y^{\ell}_{[u]}=\hat{y}^{\ell}+\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u
as an equality constraint of the form

\displaystyle\big(e_{1},e_{2}\big)(x)=0\quad\text{in }L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{\ell})^{\prime}\times\mathbb{R}^{\ell},\quad x\in{\mathscr{X}},

with the operators
e_{1}:{\mathscr{X}}\to{\mathscr{P}}_{1}^{\prime}=L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{\ell})^{\prime}
,
e_{2}:{\mathscr{X}}\to{\mathscr{P}}_{2}=\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
given by

\displaystyle{\langle e_{1}(x),\mathrm{q}^{\ell}\rangle}_{{\mathscr{P}}_{1}^{\prime},{\mathscr{P}}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\big({\bm{M}}^{\ell}(\psi)\dot{\mathrm{y}}^{\ell}(t)+{\bm{A}}^{\ell}(t;\psi)\mathrm{y}^{\ell}(t)-\mathrm{g}^{\ell}(t;u,\psi)\Big)^{\top}\mathrm{q}^{\ell}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle{\langle e_{2}(x),\mathrm{q}_{\circ}\rangle}_{{\mathscr{P}}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\big(\mathrm{y}^{\ell}(0)-\mathrm{y}_{\circ}^{\ell}(\psi)\big)^{\top}\mathrm{q}_{\circ}^{\ell}

for every
(\mathrm{q}^{\ell},q_{\circ})\in{\mathscr{P}}_{1}\times{\mathscr{P}}_{2}
. In contrast to (
3.3.7
) – we also indicate the dependence on the state variable
\psi
. The expression
y_{[u]}=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u
is based on (
4.1.2
). Thus, we define the operators
e_{3}:{\mathscr{X}}\to{\mathscr{P}}_{3}^{\prime}=L^{2}(0,T;V)^{\prime}
,
e_{4}:{\mathscr{X}}\to{\mathscr{P}}_{4}=H
by

\displaystyle{\langle e_{3}(x),q\rangle}_{{\mathscr{P}}_{3}^{\prime},{\mathscr{P}}_{3}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}{\langle(y_{t}-\mathcal{F}-\mathcal{B}u)(t),q(t)\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}+a(t;y(t),q(t))\,\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle{\langle e_{4}(x),q_{\circ}\rangle}_{{\mathscr{P}}_{4}}

\displaystyle={\langle y(0)-y_{\circ},q_{\circ}\rangle}_{H}

for every
(q,q_{\circ})\in{\mathscr{P}}_{3}\times{\mathscr{P}}_{4}
. The adjoint variable
p_{[u]}=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}u
satisfies (
4.1.12
). Therefore, we introduce the operators
e_{3}:{\mathscr{X}}\to{\mathscr{P}}_{5}^{\prime}=L^{2}(0,T;V)^{\prime}
,
e_{4}:{\mathscr{X}}\to{\mathscr{P}}_{6}=H
by

\displaystyle{\langle e_{5}(x),\xi\rangle}_{{\mathscr{P}}_{5}^{\prime},{\mathscr{P}}_{5}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}-{\langle p_{t}(t),\xi(t)\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}+a(t;\xi(t),p(t))-\sigma_{1}\,{\langle({y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}-y)(t),\xi(t)\rangle}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle{\langle e_{6}(x),\xi_{\circ}\rangle}_{{\mathscr{P}}_{6}}

\displaystyle={\langle p(T)-\sigma_{2}\,({y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-y(T)),\xi_{\circ}\rangle}_{H}

for all
(\xi,\xi_{\circ})\in{\mathscr{P}}_{5}\times{\mathscr{P}}_{6}
. To incorporate (
4.4.1
) we define the operator
e_{7}:{\mathscr{X}}\to{\mathscr{P}}_{7}=\mathbb{X}^{\ell}
as

\displaystyle{\langle e_{7}(z),\mu\rangle}_{{\mathscr{P}}_{7}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}{\langle\mathcal{R}(y,p)\psi_{i}-\lambda_{i}\psi_{i},\mu_{i}\rangle}_{X}

for every
\mu\in{\mathscr{P}}_{7}
. Notice, that we indicate the dependence of the operator
\mathcal{R}
on the state
y
and the dual
p
. Finally, we have to ensure that the POD basis is orthonormal. Here we make use of the following assumption.

Assumption 4.4.1
.

The first largest eigenvalues of
\mathcal{R}(y,p)
satisfy
\lambda_{1}>\ldots>\lambda_{\ell}
and

\displaystyle\min\big\{\lambda_{\ell}(\mathcal{R}(y,p)\,\big|\,y=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u,~p=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}u\text{ for }u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}\big\}>0.

It follows from Assumption
4.4.1
and Corollary
2.2.5
-1) that
\langle\psi_{j},\psi_{i}\rangle_{X}=\delta_{ij}
holds for
1\leq i,j\leq\ell
. Hence, we only have to ensure
\|\psi_{i}\|_{X}=1
for
1\leq i\leq\ell
. Thus, we define the operator
e_{8}:{\mathscr{X}}\to{\mathscr{P}}_{8}=\mathbb{X}^{\ell}
by

\displaystyle{\langle e_{8}(z),\eta\rangle}_{{\mathscr{P}}_{8}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\big({\|\psi_{i}\|}_{X}^{2}-1\big)\eta_{i}

for
\eta\in{\mathscr{P}}_{8}
. Setting
{\mathscr{P}}={\mathscr{P}}_{1}\times\ldots\times{\mathscr{P}}_{8}
and
e=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{8}):{\mathscr{X}}\to{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime}
the equality constraints of (

P
ℓ

os

) can be written as

\displaystyle e(x)=0\quad\text{in }{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime},\quad x\in{\mathscr{X}}.

The inequality constraints for the control variable
u
are taken into account by defining the admissible set

\displaystyle{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}=\big\{x=(\mathrm{y}^{\ell},y,p,\psi,\lambda,u)\in{\mathscr{X}}\,\big|\,u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}\text{ and }e(x)=0\text{ in }{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime}\big\},

where we identify the dual space
{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime}
with the space

\displaystyle{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime}\simeq{\mathscr{P}}_{1}^{\prime}\times{\mathscr{P}}_{2}\times{\mathscr{P}}_{3}^{\prime}\times{\mathscr{P}}_{4}\times{\mathscr{P}}_{5}^{\prime}\times{\mathscr{P}}_{6}\times{\mathscr{P}}_{7}\times{\mathscr{P}}_{8}.

It follows that
{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is weakly closed. Now we can express (

P
ℓ

os

) equivalently as the following infinite-dimensional constrained minimization problem:

\min J(y^{\ell},u)\quad\text{s.t.}\quad x=(\mathrm{y}^{\ell},y,p,\psi,\lambda,u)\in{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

(
\mathbf{P}^{\ell}_{\mathsf{os}}
)

4.4.1
Existence of optimal solutions

We make use of the following regularity assumption.

Assumption 4.4.2
.

The subspace

\displaystyle\big\{(y,p)\in W(0,T)\times W(0,T)\,\big|\,y=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u,~p=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}u\text{ for }u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}\big\}

is compactly embedded in
L^{2}(0,T;V)\times L^{2}(0,T;V)
. Aubin’s lemma implies that

Remark 4.4.3
.

In Example
3.2.8
we have presented a setting where the state space

\displaystyle\big\{y\in W(0,T)\,\big|\,y=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u\text{ for }u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}\big\}

is compactly embedded into
L^{2}(0,T;V)
. Let Assumption
4.1.8
hold. An analogous result for the dual variable
p
holds provided we even have
{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\in V
.
\blacksquare

In the next theorem we state existence of an optimal solution to (

P
ℓ

os

). For a proof we refer the reader to Section
4.5.4
.

Theorem 4.4.4
.

Let Assumptions
3.2.1
,
4.1.8
,
4.4.1
and
4.4.2
hold. Then (

P
ℓ

os

) admits a globally optimal solution
\bar{x}
.

4.4.2
Practical realization of optimality system POD

As we have already stated, (

P
ℓ

os

) is more complicated than the original problem (

^
P

). Thus we have to explain how optimality system can be used efficiently in a computational realization. In Sections
4.2.4
and
4.3.6
we have discussed a-posteriori error estimates and presented a strategy in Algorithm 4.2.1. The goal is to improve the quality of the POD approximation by including more POD basis functions in the POD Galerkin discretization. Due to the convergence results (cf. Theorems
4.2.14
and
4.3.11
) we know that – at least theoretically – for increasing
\ell
the error between the exact (unknown) control and the associated suboptimal POD control should tend to zero. However, since all POD matrices are usually dense, the POD approximations become more and more ill-conditioned as
\ell
gets bigger. Moreover, we are often not interested to involve too many POD basis functions on the POD Galerkin approximation. To overcome this problem while still ensuring a sufficient accurate approximation we apply updates of the whole POD basis. Optimality system POD offers the possibility to update the POD basis with respect to the minimization goal. In Algorithm 4.4.2 we propose a strategy which combines optimality system POD with the a-posteriori error analysis. In a first phase of the algorithm a few gradient steps for (

P
ℓ

os

) are applied in order to get a “good” POD basis and then fix this POD basis and enlarge
\ell
if necessary.

0:

Maximal number
\ell_{\mathsf{max}}
of POD basis elements,
\ell<\ell_{\mathsf{max}}
, initial control
u^{0}
, and a-posteriori error tolerance
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{apo}}>0
;

1:

Determine the state
y^{0}=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u^{0}
and adjoint
p^{0}=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}u^{0}
;

2:

Compute a POD basis
\{\psi_{i}(u^{0})\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
utilizing the two snapshots
y^{1}=y
and
y^{2}=p
;

3:

Perform
k\geq 0
(projected) gradient steps with an Armijo line search for (

P
ℓ

os

) in order to get
u^{k}
and associated POD basis
\{\psi_{i}(u^{k})\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
;

4:

Solve (

^
P

ℓ

) for
\bar{u}^{\ell}
utilizing the fixed POD basis
\{\psi_{i}(u^{k})\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
;

5:

Compute the perturbation
\zeta=\zeta(\bar{u}^{\ell})
as explained in Section
4.2.4
;

6:

if

\|\zeta\|_{\mathscr{U}}/\sigma>\varepsilon_{\mathsf{apo}}

and

\ell<\ell_{\mathrm{max}}

then

7:

Enlarge
\ell
and go back to step 4;

8:

end

if

4.5
Proofs of Section
4

4.5.1
Proofs of Section
4.1

Proof of Lemma
4.1.11
.

1)

The cost functional
J:{\mathscr{X}}\to\mathbb{R}
is convex if we have

J(\tau x+(1-\tau)\tilde{x})\leq\tau J(x)+(1-\tau)J(\tilde{x})

(4.5.1)

for every
\tau\in(0,1)
,
x=(y,u)\in{\mathscr{X}}
and
\tilde{x}=(\tilde{y},\tilde{u})\in{\mathscr{X}}
. From (
4.1.4
) we infer that

\displaystyle J(\tau x+(1-\tau)\tilde{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{1}}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\tau^{2}\,{\|y(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\sigma_{2}\tau^{2}}{2}\,{\|y(T)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{\sigma\tau^{2}}{2}\,{\|u-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\sigma_{1}}{2}\int_{0}^{T}(1-\tau)^{2}\,{\|\tilde{y}(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\sigma_{2}(1-\tau)^{2}}{2}\,{\|\tilde{y}(T)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\sigma(1-\tau)^{2}}{2}\,{\|\tilde{u}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\sigma_{1}\tau(1-\tau)\int_{0}^{T}{\langle y(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t),\tilde{y}(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)\rangle}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad+\sigma_{2}\tau(1-\tau)\,{\langle y(T)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}},\tilde{y}(T)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\rangle}_{H}+\sigma\tau(1-\tau)\,{\langle u-{u^{\mathsf{n}}},\tilde{u}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}.

From

\displaystyle{\langle y(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t),\tilde{y}(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)\rangle}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left({\|y(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}+{\|\tilde{y}(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle{\langle y(T)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}},\tilde{y}(T)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left({\|y(T)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}^{2}+{\|\tilde{y}(T)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle{\langle u-{u^{\mathsf{n}}},\tilde{u}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left({\|u-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}+{\|\tilde{u}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}\right)

and from
\tau^{2}+\tau(1-\tau)=\tau
,
(1-\tau)^{2}+\tau(1-\tau)=1-\tau
we infer that

\displaystyle J(\tau x+(1-\tau)\tilde{x})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sigma_{1}}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\tau\,{\|y(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\sigma_{2}\tau}{2}\,{\|y(T)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{\sigma\tau}{2}\,{\|u-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\sigma_{1}}{2}\int_{0}^{T}(1-\tau)\,{\|\tilde{y}(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\sigma_{2}(1-\tau)}{2}\,{\|\tilde{y}(T)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\sigma(1-\tau)}{2}\,{\|\tilde{u}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}=\tau J(x)+(1-\tau)J(\tilde{x})

for every
\tau\in(0,1)
,
x=(y,u)\in{\mathscr{X}}
and
\tilde{x}=(\tilde{y},\tilde{u})\in{\mathscr{X}}
, which is (
4.5.1
).

2)

J
is convex by part 1) and
{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is convex by assumption. Let
\bar{x}\in{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
be a local solution to (
P
) and assume that there is another admissible point
\hat{x}\in{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
with
J(\hat{x})<J(\bar{x})
. Since
{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is convex, we have
x_{\tau}:=\tau\hat{x}+(1-\tau)\bar{x}\in{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
for every
\tau\in(0,1)
as well. By convexity of
J
, we get

\displaystyle J(x_{\tau})\leq\tau J(\hat{x})+(1-\tau)J(\bar{x})<J(\bar{x})

Also,
x_{\tau}\to\bar{x}
as
\tau\searrow 0
which contradicts the fact that
\bar{x}
is a local minimizer of
J
. Therefore, it holds
J(\bar{x})\leq J(\hat{x})
for all
\hat{x}\in{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
, so
\bar{x}
is indeed a global solution of (
P
).
\Box

Proof of Theorem
4.1.16
.

Let us choose the Hilbert spaces
{\mathscr{H}}=L^{2}(0,T;H)\times H
and
{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}={\mathscr{U}}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{C}_{1}:{\mathscr{Y}}\to L^{2}(0,T;H)
is the canonical embedding operator, which is linear and bounded. We define the linear operator
\mathcal{C}_{2}:{\mathscr{Y}}\to H
by
\mathcal{C}_{2}\varphi=\varphi({t_{\mathsf{e}}})
for
\varphi\in{\mathscr{Y}}
. Since
{\mathscr{Y}}
is continuously embedded into
C([0,T];H)
, the linear operator
\mathcal{C}_{2}
is continuous. Finally, we set

\mathcal{G}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sqrt{\sigma_{1}}\,\mathcal{C}_{1}\mathcal{S}\\
begin{align*}1mm]\sqrt{\sigma_{2}}\,\mathcal{C}_{2}\mathcal{S}\end{array}\right)\in\mathscr{L}({\tilde{\mathscr{U}}},{\mathscr{H}}),\quad{y^{\mathsf{d}}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sqrt{\sigma_{1}}\,({y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}-\hat{y})\\[2.84526pt]
\sqrt{\sigma_{2}}\,\big({y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-\hat{y}({t_{\mathsf{e}}})\big)\end{array}\right)\in{\mathscr{H}},\quad\tilde{u}^{\mathsf{n}}={u^{\mathsf{n}}}\in{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}

(4.5.2)

and
{{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}={{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
. Then (

^
P

) and (
4.1.5
) coincide. Consequently, by Theorem
4.1.14
the problem (

^
P

) possesses a solution. If
\sigma>0
we can directly conclude from Theorem
4.1.14
that the solution is unique. If
\sigma_{1}>0
, we can conclude from the fact that the operator
\mathcal{S}
is injective (cf. Remark
4.1.4
-2)) that
\mathcal{G}
is injective as well. Thus, the solution is also unique in this case by Theorem
4.1.14
.
\Box

Proof of Lemma
4.1.18
.
First we compute the directional derivative of
\mathcal{J}
for a given
u\in{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}
. Let
u^{\delta}\in{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}
be an arbitrary direction and
\varepsilon>0
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(u+\varepsilon u^{\delta})-\mathcal{J}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,{\|\mathcal{G}u-{y^{\mathsf{d}}}+\varepsilon\mathcal{G}u^{\delta}\|}_{\mathscr{H}}^{2}+\frac{\sigma}{2}\,{\|u-\tilde{u}^{\mathsf{n}}+\varepsilon u^{\delta}\|}_{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\,{\|\mathcal{G}u-{y^{\mathsf{d}}}\|}_{\mathscr{H}}^{2}+\frac{\sigma}{2}\,{\|u-\tilde{u}^{\mathsf{n}}\|}_{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\,{\|\mathcal{G}u^{\delta}\|}_{\mathscr{H}}^{2}+\varepsilon\,{\langle\mathcal{G}u-{y^{\mathsf{d}}},\mathcal{G}u^{\delta}\rangle}_{\mathscr{H}}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}\sigma}{2}\,{\|u^{\delta}\|}_{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}^{2}+\varepsilon\sigma\,{\langle u-\tilde{u}^{\mathsf{n}},u^{\delta}\rangle}_{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle D\mathcal{J}(u;u^{\delta})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\bigg(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\,{\|\mathcal{G}u^{\delta}\|}_{\mathscr{H}}^{2}+\,{\langle\mathcal{G}u-{y^{\mathsf{d}}},\mathcal{G}u^{\delta}\rangle}_{\mathscr{H}}+\frac{\varepsilon\sigma}{2}\,{\|u^{\delta}\|}_{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}^{2}+\sigma\,{\langle u-\tilde{u}^{\mathsf{n}},u^{\delta}\rangle}_{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}\bigg)

\displaystyle={\langle\mathcal{G}u-{y^{\mathsf{d}}},\mathcal{G}u^{\delta}\rangle}_{\mathscr{H}}+\sigma\,{\langle u-\tilde{u}^{\mathsf{n}},u^{\delta}\rangle}_{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}.

Next we show that
D\mathcal{J}(u;\cdot):{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}\to\mathbb{R}
is already the Gâteaux derivative of
\mathcal{J}
at
u\in{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}
. Since
\mathcal{G}
is continuous on
{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}
, the mapping
D\mathcal{J}(u;\cdot):{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}\to\mathbb{R}
is well-defined for every
u\in{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle{\|D\mathcal{J}(u;\cdot)\|}_{{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|u^{\delta}\|_{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}=1}D\mathcal{J}(u;u^{\delta})=\sup_{\|u^{\delta}\|_{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}=1}\big({\langle\mathcal{G}u-{y^{\mathsf{d}}},\mathcal{G}u^{\delta}\rangle}_{\mathscr{H}}+\sigma\,{\langle u-\tilde{u}^{\mathsf{n}},u^{\delta}\rangle}_{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\|u^{\delta}\|_{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}=1}\big({\|\mathcal{G}u-{y^{\mathsf{d}}}\|}_{\mathscr{H}}{\|\mathcal{G}u^{\delta}\|}_{\mathscr{H}}+\sigma\,{\|u-\tilde{u}^{\mathsf{n}}\|}_{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}{\|u^{\delta}\|}_{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq{\|\mathcal{G}u-{y^{\mathsf{d}}}\|}_{\mathscr{H}}{\|\mathcal{G}\|}_{\mathscr{L}({\tilde{\mathscr{U}}},{\mathscr{H}})}+\sigma\,{\|u-\tilde{u}^{\mathsf{n}}\|}_{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}<\infty

by assumption. Thus,
D\mathcal{J}(u;\cdot)\in{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}^{\prime}=\mathscr{L}({\tilde{\mathscr{U}}},\mathbb{R})
holds. We conclude that the Gateaux derivative of
\mathcal{J}
at
u
is given as
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}(u)=D\mathcal{J}(u;\cdot)
.
\Box

Proof of Theorem
4.1.20
.

Let
\bar{u}\in{{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
be a solution to (
4.1.5
) and
u\in{{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
be chosen arbitrarily. Since
{{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is convex, the element
\bar{u}+\varepsilon(u-\bar{u})=\varepsilon u+(1-\varepsilon)\bar{u}
belongs to
{{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
. Since
\bar{u}
is optimal, we also have

\mathcal{J}(\bar{u}+\varepsilon(u-\bar{u}))-\mathcal{J}(\bar{u})\geq 0\quad\text{for any }\varepsilon\in(0,1].

(4.5.3)

Utilizing (
4.5.3
) and the fact that
\mathcal{J}
is Gâteaux-differentiable at
\bar{u}
, we derive

\displaystyle{\langle\nabla\mathcal{J}(\bar{u}),u-\bar{u}\rangle}_{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}=D\mathcal{J}(\bar{u},u-\bar{u})=\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\big(\mathcal{J}(\bar{u}+\varepsilon(u-\bar{u}))-\mathcal{J}(\bar{u})\big)\geq 0

which is (
4.1.8
). Let now reversely
\bar{u}\in{{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
be a control that satisfies (
4.1.8
). As the function
\mathcal{J}
is convex it holds for an arbitrary
u\in{{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(u)\geq\mathcal{J}(\bar{u})+{\langle\nabla\mathcal{J}(\bar{u}),u-\bar{u}\rangle}_{\tilde{\mathscr{U}}}\geq\mathcal{J}(\bar{u}),

where the first inequality is a well-known property of convex functions and we used (
4.1.8
) for the second inequality. Thus,
\bar{u}
is a solution to (
4.1.5
).
\Box

Proof of Lemma
4.1.22
.

1)

For
t\in[0,T]
we set

\displaystyle\tilde{a}(t;\varphi,\phi)=a(T-t;\phi,\varphi)\quad\text{for all }\varphi,\phi\in V.

Clearly, for almost all
t\in[0,T]
the bilinear form
\tilde{a}(t;\cdot\,,\cdot):V\times V\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies

\displaystyle\big|\tilde{a}(t;\varphi,\psi)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}{\|\psi\|}_{V}

\displaystyle\text{for all }\varphi,\psi\in V,

(4.5.4a)

\displaystyle\tilde{a}(t;\varphi,\varphi)

\displaystyle\geq\gamma_{1}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{V}^{2}

\displaystyle\text{for all }\varphi\in V

(4.5.4b)

for the constants
\gamma\geq 0
and
\gamma_{1}>0
introduced in (
4.1.1
). Utilizing
\mathcal{S}u\in{\mathscr{Y}}\hookrightarrow L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})
we can argue as in the proof of Theorem
3.2.4
and conclude that there exists a unique solution
\tilde{p}\in{\mathscr{Y}}
to

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle\tilde{p}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+\tilde{a}(t;\tilde{p}(t),\varphi)

\displaystyle=-\sigma_{1}\,{\langle(\mathcal{S}u)(T-t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle\text{for all }\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in}[0,T),

(4.5.5)

\displaystyle\tilde{p}(T)

\displaystyle=-\sigma_{2}\,(\mathcal{S}u)(T)

\displaystyle\text{in }H.

Moreover, a constant
C>0
exists with

{\|\tilde{p}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq C\,{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}.

(4.5.6)

Clearly,
\tilde{p}
is the solution of (
4.5.5
) if and only if
p
defined by
p(t):=\tilde{p}(T-t)
for
t\in[0,T]
is the solution to (
4.1.10
), i.e. (
4.1.10
) is also uniquely solvable. From (
4.5.6
) and
\|\tilde{p}\|_{\mathscr{Y}}=\|p\|_{\mathscr{Y}}
the claim follows.

2)

We derive from (
4.1.10
),
y=\mathcal{S}u
,
w=\mathcal{S}v
,
p=\mathcal{A}v
,
y\in W_{0}(0,T)
and integration by parts (
A.4.7
):

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}{\langle(\mathcal{B}u)(t),p(t)\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}{\langle y_{t}(t),p(t)\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}+a(t;y(t),p(t))\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}-{\langle p_{t}(t),y(t)\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}+a(t;y(t),p(t))\,\mathrm{d}t+{\langle p({t_{\mathsf{e}}}),y({t_{\mathsf{e}}})\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{T}\sigma_{1}\,{\langle w(t),y(t)\rangle}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}t-\sigma_{2}\,{\langle w(T),y(T)\rangle}_{H}

which is the claim.
\Box

Proof of Lemma
4.1.24
.

1)

Utilizing the definition of
\mathcal{G}
, we obtain for all
h\in\tilde{\mathscr{U}}
:

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{G}u-{y^{\mathsf{d}}},\mathcal{G}h\rangle}_{\mathscr{H}}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\,{\langle\mathcal{S}u-({y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}-\hat{y}),\mathcal{S}h\rangle}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}+\sigma_{2}\,{\langle(\mathcal{S}u)(T)-({y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-\hat{y}(T)),(\mathcal{S}h)(T)\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\,{\langle\mathcal{S}u,\mathcal{S}h\rangle}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}+\sigma_{2}\,{\langle(\mathcal{S}u)(T),(\mathcal{S}h)(T)\rangle}_{H}-\sigma_{1}\,{\langle{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}-\hat{y},\mathcal{S}h\rangle}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}

\displaystyle\quad-\sigma_{2}\,{\langle{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-\hat{y}(T),(\mathcal{S}h)(T)\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle={\langle\Theta\mathcal{S}u,\mathcal{S}h\rangle}_{W_{0}(0,T)^{\prime},W_{0}(0,T)}-{\langle\Xi(y_{1}^{\mathsf{d}}-\hat{y},y_{2}^{\mathsf{d}}-\hat{y}(T)),\mathcal{S}h\rangle}_{\mathscr{H}}

Note that in the first summand, we understand
\mathcal{S}
as a mapping from
{\mathscr{U}}
to
W_{0}(0,T)
whereas in the second, it is considered to map to
{\mathscr{H}}
. In both cases, the dual operator
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
maps to
\tilde{U}^{\prime}\simeq\tilde{U}
which directly gives (
4.1.14
).

2)

Let
u,v\in\mathscr{U}
be chosen arbitrarily. We set
y=\mathcal{S}u\in W_{0}(0,T)
and
w=\mathcal{S}v\in W_{0}(0,T)
. Recall that we identify
{\mathscr{U}}
with its dual space
{\mathscr{U}}^{\prime}
. From the integration by parts formula and Lemma
4.1.22
-2 we infer that

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\Theta\mathcal{S}v,u\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}

\displaystyle={\langle\Theta\mathcal{S}v,\mathcal{S}u\rangle}_{W_{0}(0,T)^{\prime},W_{0}(0,T)}={\langle\Theta w,y\rangle}_{W_{0}(0,T)^{\prime},W_{0}(0,T)}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\sigma_{1}\,{\langle w(t),y(t)\rangle}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{2}\,{\langle w(T),y(T)\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle=-{\langle\mathcal{B}u,p\rangle}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime}),L^{2}(0,T;V)}=-{\langle u,\mathcal{B}^{\prime}p\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}=-{\langle\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\mathcal{A}v,u\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}.

Since
u,v\in{\mathscr{U}}
are chosen arbitrarily, we have
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\mathcal{A}=-\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\Theta\mathcal{S}
. Further, we find utilizing (
4.1.11
)

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\Xi({y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}-\hat{y},{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-\hat{y}(T)),u\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}={\langle\Xi({y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}-\hat{y},{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-\hat{y}(T)),\mathcal{S}u\rangle}_{W_{0}(0,T)^{\prime},W_{0}(0,T)}

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{0}^{T}\sigma_{1}\,{\langle{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}-\hat{y}(t),y(t)\rangle}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{2}\,{\langle{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-\hat{y}(T),y(T)\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{0}^{T}-{\langle\hat{p}_{t}(t),y(t)\rangle}_{H}+a(t;y(t),\hat{p}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t+{\langle\hat{p}(T),y(T)\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{0}^{T}{\langle y_{t}(t),\hat{p}(t)\rangle}_{H}+a(t;y(t),\hat{p}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{T}{\langle(\mathcal{B}u)(t),\hat{p}(t)\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\,\mathrm{d}t={\langle\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\hat{p},u\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}},

which gives the claim since
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
was chosen arbitrarily.
\Box

Proof of Lemma
4.1.28
.
Let
x=(y,u)\in{\mathscr{X}}
and
x^{\delta}=(y^{\delta},u^{\delta})\in{\mathscr{X}}
be given arbitrarily. Then

\displaystyle J(x+x^{\delta})-J(x)-J^{\prime}(x)x^{\delta}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{1}}{2}\int_{0}^{T}{\|y(t)+y^{\delta}(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\sigma_{2}}{2}\,{\|y(T)+y^{\delta}(T)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{\sigma}{2}\,{\|u+u^{\delta}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\sigma_{1}}{2}\int_{0}^{T}{\|y(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t-\frac{\sigma_{2}}{2}\,{\|y(T)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}^{2}-\frac{\sigma}{2}\,{\|u-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-\sigma_{1}\int_{0}^{T}{\langle y(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t),y^{\delta}(t)\rangle}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}t-\sigma_{2}\,{\langle y(T)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}},y^{\delta}(T)\rangle}_{H}-\sigma\,{\langle u-{u^{\mathsf{n}}},u^{\delta}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{1}}{2}\,{\|y^{\delta}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}^{2}+\frac{\sigma_{2}}{2}\,{\|y^{\delta}(T)\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{\sigma}{2}\,{\|u^{\delta}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}.

Since
{\mathscr{Y}}
is continuously embedded into
L^{2}(0,T;H)
and
C([0,T];H)
, respectively, there exists a constant
c_{1}>0
independent of
y^{\delta}
, satisfying

\displaystyle\max\big({\|y^{\delta}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)},{\|y^{\delta}(T)\|}_{H}\big)\leq c_{1}\,{\|y^{\delta}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\big|J(x+x^{\delta})-J(x)-J^{\prime}(x)x^{\delta}\big|\leq c_{2}\big({\|y^{\delta}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}+{\|u^{\delta}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}\big)=c_{2}\,{\|x^{\delta}\|}_{\mathscr{X}}^{2}

with
c_{2}=\max(c_{1}^{2}\sigma_{1},c_{1}^{2}\sigma_{2},\sigma)/2
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\|x^{\delta}\|_{\mathscr{X}}}\,\big|J(x+x^{\delta})-J(x)-J^{\prime}(x)x^{\delta}\big|\leq c_{2}\|x^{\delta}\|_{\mathscr{X}}\to 0\quad\text{as }x^{\delta}\to 0

which implies that
J
is Fréchet-differentiable in
x
and its Fréchet-derivative is given by (
4.1.21
). For the continuity of the mapping
{\mathscr{X}}\ni x\mapsto J^{\prime}(x)\in{\mathscr{X}}^{\prime}
, consider that an alternative way to represent the derivative
J^{\prime}(x)\in\mathscr{X}^{\prime}
is

\displaystyle J^{\prime}(x)x^{\delta}=\sigma_{1}\,{\langle y-y_{1}^{\mathsf{d}},y^{\delta}\rangle}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}+\sigma_{2}\,{\langle y(T)-y_{2}^{\mathsf{d}},y^{\delta}_{2}(T)\rangle}_{H}+\sigma\,{\langle u-{u^{\mathsf{n}}},u^{\delta}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}

From this representation, the continuity can be easily shown if we consider that the embeddings
{\mathscr{Y}}\hookrightarrow L^{2}(0,T;H)
,
{\mathscr{Y}}\hookrightarrow C([0,T];H)
as well as the inner products are continuous.
\Box

Proof of Lemma
4.1.30
.
The assertion follows from the fact that
{\mathscr{X}}\ni x\mapsto e(x)\in\Lambda^{\prime}
is a bounded, affine-linear operator. More precisely, using (
4.1.18
) and (
4.1.22
) we infer that

\displaystyle{\langle e_{1}(x+x^{\delta})-e_{1}(x)-e_{1}^{\prime}(x)x^{\delta},p^{1}\rangle}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime}),L^{2}(0,T;V)}=0

and

\displaystyle{\langle e_{2}(x+x^{\delta})-e_{2}(x)-e_{2}^{\prime}(x)x^{\delta},p^{2}\rangle}_{H}=0

for all
x=(y,u)\in{\mathscr{X}}
,
x^{\delta}=(y^{\delta},u^{\delta})\in X
and
p=(p^{1},p^{2})\in\Lambda
. Thus,

\displaystyle{\|e(x+x^{\delta})-e(x)-e^{\prime}(x)x^{\delta}\|}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|p\|_{\Lambda}=1}\Big({\langle e_{1}(x+x^{\delta})-e_{1}(x)-e_{1}^{\prime}(x)x^{\delta},p^{1}\rangle}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime}),L^{2}(0,T;V)}

\displaystyle\hskip 51.21495pt+{\langle e_{2}(x+x^{\delta})-e_{2}(x)-e_{2}^{\prime}(x)x^{\delta},p^{2}\rangle}_{H}\Big)=0.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\lim_{\|x^{\delta}\|_{\mathscr{X}}\to 0}\frac{\|e(x+x^{\delta})-e(x)-e^{\prime}(x)x^{\delta}\|_{\Lambda^{\prime}}}{\|x^{\delta}\|_{\mathscr{X}}}=0

holds. The continuity of the mapping
{\mathscr{X}}\ni x\mapsto e^{\prime}(x)
is trivial because
e^{\prime}(x)
does not depend on
x
.
\Box

Proof of Proposition
4.1.33
.

\Rightarrow
:

Suppose that (
4.1.24
) holds, i.e. for every
y^{\delta}\in{\mathscr{Y}}
, we have

\displaystyle 0=\mathcal{L}_{y}(x(u),p(u))y^{\delta}=J_{y}(x(u))y^{\delta}+\langle e_{y}(x(u))y^{\delta},p(u)\rangle_{\Lambda^{\prime},\Lambda}={\langle J_{y}(x(u))+e_{y}(x(u))^{\prime}p(u),y^{\delta}\rangle}_{{\mathscr{Y}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{Y}}}.

Then we infer that

\displaystyle e_{y}(x(u))^{\prime}p(u)=-J_{y}(x(u))\quad\text{in }{\mathscr{Y}}^{\prime}.

Since
e_{y}(x)^{\prime}
is invertible by Remark
4.1.32
, we find that
p(u)
satisfies (
4.1.25
).

\Leftarrow
:

Let the adjoint variable
p(u)=(p^{1}(u),p^{2}(u))
be given by (
4.1.25
). Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{y}(x(u),p(u))y^{\delta}

\displaystyle={\langle J_{y}(x(u))-e_{y}(x(u))^{\prime}(e_{y}(x(u))^{\prime})^{-1}J_{y}(x(u)),y^{\delta}\rangle}_{{\mathscr{Y}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{Y}}}

\displaystyle={\langle J_{y}(x(u))-J_{y}(x(u)),y^{\delta}\rangle}_{{\mathscr{Y}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{Y}}}=0.

which is (
4.1.24
).

Using
\mathcal{L}_{y}(y,u,p)=0
in
{\mathscr{Y}}^{\prime}
, Lemmas
4.1.28
and
4.1.30
we find that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\int_{0}^{T}{\langle y(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t),y^{\delta}(t)\rangle}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{2}\,{\langle y({t_{\mathsf{e}}})-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}},y^{\delta}(T)\rangle}_{H}

(4.5.7)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}{\langle y^{\delta}_{t}(t),p^{1}(t)\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}+a(t;y^{\delta}(t),p^{1}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t+{\langle y^{\delta}(0),p^{2}\rangle}_{H}

for all
y^{\delta}\in{\mathscr{Y}}
. Suppose that
y^{\delta}(t)=\chi(t)\varphi
with
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(0,T)
and
\varphi\in V
. Then
y^{\delta}\in{\mathscr{Y}}
and
y^{\delta}(0)=y^{\delta}(T)=0
hold. Moreover, we have

\int_{0}^{T}{\langle y^{\delta}_{t}(t),p^{1}(t)\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\,\mathrm{d}t=\bigg\langle\int_{0}^{T}p^{1}(t)\dot{\chi}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,\varphi\bigg\rangle_{H}=-\bigg\langle\int_{0}^{T}p^{1}_{t}(t)\chi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,\varphi\bigg\rangle_{V^{\prime},V},

(4.5.8)

where
p^{1}_{t}
stands for the distributional derivative of
p^{1}
. Inserting (
4.5.8
) into (
4.5.7
) leads to

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\Big(-{\langle p_{t}^{1}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}+a(t;\varphi,p^{1}(t))+\sigma_{1}\,{\langle y(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}\Big)\chi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=0

for all
\varphi\in V
and
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(0,T)
. Since
C_{0}^{\infty}(0,T)
is dense in
L^{2}(0,T)
we get

\displaystyle-{\langle p^{1}_{t}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}+a(t;\varphi,p^{1}(t))+\sigma_{1}\,{\langle y(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}=0\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in }[0,T).

Note that for almost all
t\in[0,T]
we have

\displaystyle a(t;\cdot\,,p^{1}(t))+\sigma_{1}\,{\langle y^{1}(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t),\cdot\rangle}_{H}\in V^{\prime}.

Hence,
p^{1}_{t}\in L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})
, which implies
p^{1}\in{\mathscr{Y}}
and
