\displaystyle{\langle p^{1}_{t}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle p^{1}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}\quad\forall\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in }[0,T].

Thus, we have derived

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle p^{1}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\varphi,p^{1}(t))=\sigma_{1}\,{\langle{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)-y(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in }[0,T)

which coincides with (
4.1.12a
). Combining this and (
4.5.7
) yields:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\int_{0}^{T}{\langle y(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t),y^{\delta}(t)\rangle}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{2}\,{\langle y(T)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}},y^{\delta}(T)\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle\quad-\sigma_{1}\int_{0}^{T}\langle y_{1}^{\mathsf{d}}(t)-y(t),y^{\delta}(t)\rangle_{H}\,\mathrm{d}t+{\langle y^{\delta}(T),p^{1}(T)\rangle}_{H}-\langle y^{\delta}(0),p^{1}(0)\rangle_{H}+{\langle y^{\delta}(0),p^{2}\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle={\langle p^{1}(T)-\sigma_{2}({y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-y(T)),y^{\delta}(T)\rangle}_{H}-{\langle p^{1}(0)-p^{2},y^{\delta}(0)\rangle}_{H}

where we have used partial integration for the second interval in (
4.5.7
). Choosing
y^{\delta}
with
y^{\delta}(0)=0
implies
p^{1}(T)=\sigma_{2}(y_{2}^{\mathsf{d}}-y(T))
in
H
which is (
4.1.12b
). Furthermore, we get
p^{2}=p^{1}(0)
in
H
if we choose
y^{\delta}
with
y^{\delta}(0)=0
.
\Box

Proof of Lemma
4.1.35
.
Let
x=(y,u)\in{\mathscr{X}}
and
x^{\delta}=(y^{\delta},u^{\delta})\in{\mathscr{X}}
and
\tilde{x}^{\delta}=(\tilde{y}^{\delta},\tilde{u}^{\delta})\in{\mathscr{X}}
. Then we infer from (
4.1.21
) and (
4.1.26
) that

\displaystyle{\|J^{\prime}(x+x^{\delta})-J^{\prime}(x)-J^{\prime\prime}(x)x^{\delta}\|}_{{\mathscr{X}}^{\prime}}=\sup_{\|\tilde{x}^{\delta}\|_{\mathscr{X}}=1}{\langle J^{\prime}(x+x^{\delta})-J^{\prime}(x)-J^{\prime\prime}(x)x^{\delta},\tilde{x}^{\delta}\rangle}_{{\mathscr{X}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{X}}}=0

so that the second Fréchet derivative is given by (
4.1.26
). Since
J^{\prime\prime}(x)
is independent of
x
, we conclude that
x\mapsto J^{\prime\prime}(x)
is continuous, of course. Now we turn to the mapping
e
. It follows from (
4.1.22
) that for every
x\in{\mathscr{X}}
the linear and bounded operator
e^{\prime}(x)
is independent of
x
. Thus,
e
is twice continuously Fréchet-differentiable and its second derivative is zero.
\Box

Proof of Lemma
4.1.36
.
Let
x^{\delta}=(y^{\delta},u^{\delta})\in\mathrm{ker}\,e^{\prime}(x)

e^{\prime}(x)x^{\delta}=0\quad\text{in }{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime}.

(4.5.9)

Due to Lemma
4.1.30
we deduce that (

) is equivalent with

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}{\langle y^{\delta}_{t}(t),\varphi(t)\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}+a(t;y^{\delta}(t),\varphi(t))\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}{\langle(\mathcal{B}u^{\delta})(t),\varphi(t)\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\,\mathrm{d}t\quad\forall\varphi\in L^{2}(0,T;V),

(4.5.10)

\displaystyle y^{\delta}({t_{\circ}})

\displaystyle=0.

System (
4.5.10
) is equivalent with

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle y^{\delta}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;y^{\delta}(t),\varphi)

\displaystyle={\langle(\mathcal{B}u^{\delta})(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

(4.5.11)

\displaystyle y^{\delta}({t_{\circ}})

\displaystyle=0.

Applying Corrollary
3.2.5
-2) we find that
y^{\delta}
is uniquely determined as

. The estimate follows directly from (
