).
\Box

Proof of Proposition
4.1.37
.
Applying (
4.1.27
) and Lemma
4.1.36
we find

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{L}_{xx}(x,p)x^{\delta},x^{\delta}\rangle}_{{\mathscr{X}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{X}}}\geq\sigma\,{\|u^{\delta}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}\geq\frac{\sigma}{2}\,{\|u^{\delta}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}+\frac{\sigma}{2c_{\mathsf{ker}}}\,{\|y^{\delta}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2}\geq\kappa\,{\|x^{\delta}\|}_{\mathscr{X}}^{2}

for all
x^{\delta}\in\mathrm{ker}\,e^{\prime}(x)
with
\kappa=\tfrac{\sigma}{2}\min\{1,1/c_{\mathsf{ker}}\}>0
.

4.5.2
Proofs of Section
4.2

Proof of Corollary
4.2.4
.
For part 1), it suffices to choose
u=0
in Proposition
4.2.3
, resulting in
p^{\ell}=\hat{p}^{\ell}
. For part 2), assume that
f=0
and
y_{\circ}=0
holds in (
4.1.2
), and therefore
\hat{y}=0
. Furthermore, let
y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}=0
hold in
H
and
y_{1}^{\mathsf{d}}=0
in
L^{2}(0,T;H)
. Altogether, we then have
p^{\ell}=\mathcal{A}^{\ell}u
. Applying Proposition
4.2.3
yields (
4.2.7
). Since
\mathcal{S}^{\ell}:{\mathscr{U}}\to{\mathscr{Y}}
is continuous and
{\mathscr{Y}}
embeds continuously into
C([0,T];H)
as well as
L^{2}(0,T;H)
, there also exists
C_{1}>0
with

\displaystyle\max\left(\lVert(\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u)(T)\rVert_{H},\lVert\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u\rVert_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}\right)\leq C_{1}\|u\|_{\mathscr{U}}

A further estimation of (
4.2.7
) then results in

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{A}^{\ell}u\|_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq CC_{1}(\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{1})\|u\|_{\mathscr{U}}

which proves the boundedness of
\mathcal{A}^{\ell}
.
\Box

Proof of Corollary
4.2.6
.
Let
y^{\ell}=\hat{y}^{\ell}+\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u
. Due to (
A.4.6
) we have

{\|y^{\ell}(T)\|}_{H}\leq{\|y^{\ell}\|}_{C([0,T];H)}\leq c_{\mathscr{Y}}\,{\|y^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}.

(4.5.12)

Moreover, we infer from the embedding inequality (
2.4.1
) that

{\|y^{\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}^{2}=\int_{0}^{T}{\|y^{\ell}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{0}^{T}c_{V}^{2}\,{\|y^{\ell}(t)\|}_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t=c_{V}^{2}\,{\|y^{\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}.

(4.5.13)

Thus, using Proposition
4.2.3
, triangle inequality, (
4.2.3
), (
4.5.12
) and (
4.5.13
) we deduce

\displaystyle{\|p^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\sigma_{2}\big({\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}+{\|y^{\ell}(T)\|}_{H}\big)+\sigma_{1}\big({\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}+{\|y^{\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}\big)\right)

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\big({\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}+{\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}\big)+c_{2}\,{\|y^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}\left({\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}+{\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}+{\|y_{\circ}\|}_{H}+{\|f\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}+{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\right)

with
c_{1}=C\max(\sigma_{2},\sigma_{1})
,
c_{2}=C(\sigma_{2}C_{\mathscr{Y}}+\sigma_{1}c_{V})
and
c_{3}=\max(c_{1},c_{2}C_{y})
.
\Box

Proof of Lemma
4.2.7
.
From
p,p^{\ell}\in H^{1}(0,T;V)
we infer that
\theta^{\ell}\in H^{1}(0,T;V)
and

{\langle\theta^{\ell}_{t}(t),\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}={\langle\theta^{\ell}_{t}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}\quad\text{for all }\psi\in V\text{ in }[0,T]\text{ a.e.}

(4.5.14)

Moreover, we have (cf. (
3.8.20
))

{\langle\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p_{t}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}={\langle p_{t}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}\quad\text{for all }\psi\in X^{\ell}\text{ in }[0,T]\text{ a.e.}

(4.5.15)

From (
4.1.12
), (
4.2.5
), (
4.5.15
), (
4.5.14
) and (
4.1.1a
) we obtain that

\displaystyle-{\langle\theta^{\ell}_{t}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\psi,\theta^{\ell}(t))

\displaystyle=-{\langle p_{t}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\psi,\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p(t))+{\langle p_{t}^{\ell}(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}-a(t;\psi,p^{\ell}(t))

\displaystyle=a(t;\psi,(\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p-p)(t))+\sigma_{1}\,{\langle(y^{\ell}-y)(t),\psi\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\,{\|\psi\|}_{V}{\|(\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p-p)(t)\|}_{V}+\sigma_{1}\,{\|(y^{\ell}-y)(t)\|}_{H}{\|\psi\|}_{H}

in
[0,T]
a.e. Choosing
\psi=\theta^{\ell}(t)\in X^{\ell}
and using (
4.1.1b
) we find

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\|\theta^{\ell}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}+\gamma_{1}\,{\|\theta^{\ell}(t)\|}_{V}^{2}\leq\gamma\,{\|\theta^{\ell}(t)\|}_{V}{\|(\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p-p)(t)\|}_{V}+\sigma_{1}\,{\|(y^{\ell}-y)(t)\|}_{H}{\|\theta^{\ell}(t)\|}_{H}

in
[0,T]
a.e. We apply Young’s inequality (cf. Lemma
A.1.30
with
p=q=2
and
\varepsilon=\gamma_{1}^{2}/\gamma^{2}
for the first,
\varepsilon=\sigma_{1}
for the second product):

-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\|\theta^{\ell}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}+\gamma_{1}\,{\|\theta^{\ell}(t)\|}_{V}^{2}\leq{\|\theta^{\ell}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\gamma_{1}^{2}}\,{\|(\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p-p)(t)\|}_{V}^{2}+\sigma_{1}^{2}\,{\|(y^{\ell}-y)(t)\|}_{H}^{2}

(4.5.16)

in
[0,T]
a.e. Recall that the embedding constant
c_{V}
has been introduced in (
2.4.1
). We set

\displaystyle c_{1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\text{if }1-\frac{\gamma_{1}}{c_{V}^{2}}\leq 0,\\[8.61108pt]
1-\frac{\gamma_{1}}{c_{V}^{2}}&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

and infer

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\|\theta^{\ell}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}\leq c_{1}\,{\|\theta^{\ell}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\gamma_{1}^{2}}\,{\|(\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p-p)(t)\|}_{V}^{2}+\sigma_{1}^{2}\,{\|(y^{\ell}-y)(t)\|}_{H}^{2}.

Using the Gronwall Lemma (cf. Corollary
A.1.33
), it follows that

\displaystyle{\|\theta^{\ell}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}\leq e^{c_{1}(T-t)}\bigg({\|\theta^{\ell}(T)\|}_{H}^{2}+\int_{t}^{T}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\gamma_{1}^{2}}\,{\|(\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p-p)(s)\|}_{V}^{2}+\sigma_{1}^{2}\,{\|(y^{\ell}-y)(s)\|}_{H}^{2}~\mathrm{d}s\bigg).

For the terminal value, we use the definitions of
p
and
p^{\ell}
in (
4.1.12b
) and (
4.2.5b
):

\theta^{\ell}(T)=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p(T)-p^{\ell}(T)=\sigma_{2}\big(y^{\ell}(T)-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y(T)\big).

(4.5.17)

Thus we arrive at

\displaystyle{\|\theta^{\ell}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq e^{c_{1}(T-t)}\Big(\sigma_{2}^{2}\|(y^{\ell}-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y)(T)\|_{H}^{2}+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\gamma_{1}^{2}}\,{\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p-p\|}_{L^{2}(t,T;V)}^{2}+\sigma_{1}^{2}\,{\|y^{\ell}-y\|}_{L^{2}(t,T;H)}^{2}\Big)

(4.5.18)

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}\Big({\|(y^{\ell}-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y)(T)\|}_{H}^{2}+{\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p-p\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}+{\|y^{\ell}-y\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}^{2}\Big)

with
c_{3}=e^{c_{1}T}\max(\sigma_{2}^{2},\gamma^{2}/\gamma_{1},\sigma_{1}^{2})>0
. Next, we integrate (
4.5.16
) over
[0,T]
from which it follows:

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}\,{\|\theta^{\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{\|\theta^{\ell}(T)\|}_{H}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}{\|\theta^{\ell}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\gamma_{1}^{2}}\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}-p\|_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}+\sigma_{1}^{2}\,{\|y^{\ell}-y\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}^{2}.

Using (
4.5.17
) for
\theta^{\ell}(T)
and applying (
4.5.18
), we find that

\displaystyle{\|\theta^{\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}\leq c_{4}\Big({\|(y^{\ell}-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y)(T)\|}^{2}_{H}+{\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p-p\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}+{\|y^{\ell}-y\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}^{2}\Big)

with
c_{4}=\nicefrac{{1}}{{\gamma}}\max(\sigma_{2}^{2}+c_{3}T,\gamma^{2}/\gamma_{1}^{2}+c_{3}T,\sigma_{1}^{2}+c_{3}T)
. Using (
4.5.13
) and (
A.4.6
) we get (
4.2.10
) with
c_{\theta}=c_{4}\max(c_{\mathscr{Y}}^{2},1,c_{V}^{2})
.
\Box

Proof of Theorem
4.2.8
.
Combining (
4.2.9
) and (
4.2.10
) we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}{\|p(t)-p^{\ell}(t)\|}_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\Big({\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}p-p\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}+{\|\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y-y\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}\Big)

(4.5.19)

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{k=1}^{\wp}\omega_{k}^{\wp}\int_{0}^{T}\big\|y^{k}(t)-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y^{k}(t)\big\|_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

with
C=2c_{\theta}+2
. It follows from (
2.4.10b
) that for
X=H
and
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H}
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\wp}\omega_{k}^{\wp}\int_{0}^{T}\big\|y^{k}(t)-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}y^{k}(t)\big\|_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t=\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{H}\big\|\psi_{i}^{H}\big\|_{V}^{2}<\infty.

Thus, (
4.5.19
) yields

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}{\|p(t)-p^{\ell}(t)\|}_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{H}\big\|\psi_{i}^{H}\big\|_{V}^{2}.

For the choices
X=V
and
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}
the claim follows analogously from (
4.5.19
) and (
2.4.10d
).
\Box

Proof of Theorem
4.2.12
.
Choosing
u=\bar{u}^{\ell}
in (
4.1.17b
) and
u=\bar{u}
in (
4.2.12
) we get the variational inequality

0\leq{\langle\sigma(\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\bar{p}-\bar{p}^{\ell}),\bar{u}^{\ell}-\bar{u}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}.

(4.5.20)

Utilizing Lemma
4.1.24
and
\langle\Theta\varphi,\varphi\rangle_{W_{0}(0,T)^{\prime},W_{0}(0,T)}\geq 0
for all
\varphi\in W_{0}(0,T)
we infer from (
4.5.20
) that

\displaystyle 0\leq

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\mathcal{A}^{\ell}\bar{u}^{\ell}-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\mathcal{A}\bar{u}+\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\hat{p}^{\ell}-\hat{p}),\bar{u}^{\ell}-\bar{u}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}-\sigma\,{\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\mathcal{A}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell}-\bar{u})+\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A}^{\ell}-\mathcal{A})\bar{u}+\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\hat{p}^{\ell}-\hat{p}),\bar{u}^{\ell}-\bar{u}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}-\sigma\,{\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\mathcal{A}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell}-\bar{u})+\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\hat{p}^{\ell}+\mathcal{A}^{\ell}\bar{u}-\hat{p}-\mathcal{A}\bar{u}),\bar{u}^{\ell}-\bar{u}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}-\sigma\,{\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{\langle\Theta\mathcal{S}^{\ell}(\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}),\mathcal{S}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell}-\bar{u})\rangle}_{W_{0}(0,T)^{\prime},W_{0}(0,T)}+{\|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\bar{p}^{\ell}(\bar{u})-\bar{p}(\bar{u}))\|}_{\mathscr{U}}{\|\bar{u}^{\ell}-\bar{u}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}-\sigma\,{\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\|}_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(0,T;V),{\mathscr{U}})}{\|\bar{p}^{\ell}(\bar{u})-\bar{p}(\bar{u})\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}{\|\bar{u}^{\ell}-\bar{u}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}-\sigma\,{\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2},

where we have set
\bar{p}^{\ell}(\bar{u})=\hat{p}^{\ell}+\mathcal{A}^{\ell}\bar{u}
and
\bar{p}(\bar{u})=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}\bar{u}
. Consequently,

\displaystyle{\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\leq\frac{1}{\sigma}\,{\|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\|}_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(0,T;V),{\mathscr{U}})}{\|\bar{p}^{\ell}(\bar{u})-\bar{p}(\bar{u})\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}.

Now (
4.3.23
) follows from Theorem
4.2.8
.
\Box

Proof of Theorem
4.2.14
.
Estimate (
4.2.16
) has already be shown. We proceed by constructing the function
\zeta^{\ell}
. Here we adapt the lines of the proof of Proposition 3.2 in
[
TV09
]
to our optimal control problem. Suppose that we know
\bar{u}^{\ell}
and
\tilde{p}^{\ell}=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}\bar{u}^{\ell}
. The goal is to determine
\zeta^{\ell}\in{\mathscr{U}}
satisfying (
4.2.14
). We distinguish three different cases.

\blacksquare

Case
\bar{u}^{\ell}_{i}(s)=u_{\mathsf{a}i}(s)
for fixed
s\in\mathscr{D}
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,\mathsf{m}\}
: Then
u_{i}(s)-\bar{u}_{i}^{\ell}(s)=u_{i}(s)-u_{\mathsf{a}i}(s)\geq 0
for all
u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
. Hence,
\zeta^{\ell}_{i}(s)
has to satisfy

\big(\sigma(\bar{u}^{\ell}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\tilde{p}^{\ell}\big)_{i}(s)+\zeta_{i}^{\ell}(s)\geq 0.

(4.5.21)

Setting
\zeta_{i}^{\ell}(s)=-\min(0,(\sigma(\bar{u}^{\ell}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\tilde{p}^{\ell})_{i}(s))
the value
\zeta_{i}^{\ell}(s)
satisfies (
4.5.21
).

\blacksquare

Case
\bar{u}^{\ell}_{i}(s)=u_{\mathsf{b}i}(s)
for fixed
s\in\mathscr{D}
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,\mathsf{m}\}
: Now,
u_{i}(s)-\bar{u}_{i}^{\ell}(s)=u(s)-u_{\mathsf{b}i}(s)\leq 0
for all
u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
. Analogously to the first case we define
\zeta_{i}^{\ell}(s)=-\max(0,(\sigma(\bar{u}^{\ell}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\tilde{p}^{\ell})_{i}(s))
to ensure

\big(\sigma(\bar{u}^{\ell}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\tilde{p}^{\ell}\big)_{i}(s)+\zeta_{i}^{\ell}(s)\leq 0.

(4.5.22)

\blacksquare

Case
u_{\mathsf{a}i}(s)<\bar{u}_{i}^{\ell}(s)<u_{\mathsf{b}i}(s)
for fixed
s\in\mathscr{D}
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,\mathsf{m}\}
: Consequently,

(\sigma(\bar{u}^{\ell}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\tilde{p}^{\ell})_{i}(s)+\zeta^{\ell}_{i}(s)=0

(4.5.23)

has to hold, which is guaranteed by setting
\zeta_{i}^{\ell}(s)=-(\sigma(\bar{u}^{\ell}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\tilde{p}^{\ell})_{i}(s)
.

It remains to prove that
\zeta^{\ell}
tends to zero for
\ell\to\infty
. Here we follow the proof of Theorem 4.11 in
[
TV09
]
. By Theorem
4.2.12
-1), the sequence
\{\bar{u}^{\ell}\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
\bar{u}
in
U
. Since the linear operator
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\mathcal{A}
is bounded and
\tilde{p}^{\ell}=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}\bar{u}^{\ell}
holds,
\{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\tilde{p}^{\ell}\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
tends to
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p}=\hat{p}+\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\mathcal{A}\bar{u}
as well. Furthermore, for
\bar{p}^{\ell}=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}^{\ell}\bar{u}^{\ell}
it holds

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p}^{\ell}-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p}=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A}^{\ell}\bar{u}^{\ell}-\mathcal{A}\bar{u})=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\mathcal{A}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell}-\bar{u})+\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A}^{\ell}-\mathcal{A})\bar{u}.

Since the operator
\mathcal{A}^{\ell}
is bounded independently of
\ell
, the sequence
\{\bar{u}^{\ell}\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
\bar{u}
and
\lim_{\ell\to\infty}(\mathcal{A}^{\ell}-\mathcal{A})\bar{u}=0
(due to (
4.2.11
)), we can conclude that the sequence
\{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p}^{\ell}\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p}
.

Hence, there exist subsequences
\{\bar{u}^{\ell_{k}}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
,
\{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p}^{\ell_{k}}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\tilde{p}^{\ell_{k}}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\bar{u}_{i}^{\ell_{k}}(s)

\displaystyle=\bar{u}_{i}(s)

(4.5.24a)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\tilde{p}^{\ell_{k}})_{i}(s)

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p})_{i}(s)

(4.5.24b)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p}^{\ell_{k}})_{i}(s)

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p})_{i}(s)

(4.5.24c)

f.a.a.
s\in\mathscr{D}
and for
1\leq i\leq\mathsf{m}
. Next we consider the active and inactive sets for
\bar{u}
.

\blacksquare

Let
s\in\mathscr{J}_{i}=\{s\in\mathscr{D}\,|\,u_{\mathsf{a}i}(s)<\bar{u}_{i}(s)<u_{\mathsf{b}i}(s)\}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,\mathsf{m}\}
. By using statement 2) from Proposition
4.1.27
this immediately implies that
(\sigma(\bar{u}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p})_{i}(s)=0
. Additionally, from (
4.5.24a
) we can deduce that there exists
k_{\circ}=k_{\circ}(s)\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\bar{u}^{\ell_{k}}_{i}(s)\in(u_{\mathsf{a}i}(s),u_{\mathsf{b}i}(s))
holds for all
k\geq k_{\circ}
. Thus,
\zeta^{\ell_{k}}_{i}(s)=-(\sigma(\bar{u}^{\ell_{k}}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\tilde{p}^{\ell_{k}})_{i}(s)
for all
k\geq k_{\circ}
. Now (
4.5.24a
) and (
4.5.24b
) imply

\lim_{k\to\infty}\zeta^{\ell_{k}}_{i}(s)=(\sigma(\bar{u}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p})_{i}(s)=0.

(4.5.25)

\blacksquare

Suppose that
s\in\mathscr{A}_{\mathsf{a}i}=\{s\in\mathscr{D}\,|\,u_{\mathsf{a}i}(s)=\bar{u}_{i}(s)\}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,\mathsf{m}\}
. Then it holds
(\sigma(\bar{u}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p})_{i}(s)\geq 0
. If
(\sigma(\bar{u}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p})_{i}(s)=0
we can proceed similarly to the first case and conclude that
\lim_{k\to\infty}\zeta^{\ell_{k}}_{i}(s)=0
. If
(\sigma(\bar{u}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p})_{i}(s)>0
holds, there is a
k_{\circ}=k_{\circ}(s)\in\mathbb{N}
such that
(\sigma(\bar{u}^{\ell_{k}}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p}^{\ell_{k}})_{i}(s)>0
for all
k\geq k_{\circ}
by (
4.5.24c
). Thus, Proposition
4.1.27
-2) yields
\bar{u}^{\ell_{k}}_{i}(s)=u_{\mathsf{a}i}(s)
for all
k\geq k_{\circ}
. As previously shown,
\zeta^{\ell_{k}}_{i}(s)
is then given by
\zeta^{\ell_{k}}_{i}(s)=-\min(0,(\sigma(\bar{u}^{\ell}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\tilde{p}^{\ell})_{i}(s))
. Because of (
4.5.24b
) we get

\lim_{k\to\infty}\zeta^{\ell_{k}}_{i}(s)=-\min(0,(\sigma(\bar{u}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p})_{i}(s))=0.

(4.5.26)

\blacksquare

Suppose that
s\in\mathscr{A}_{\mathsf{b}i}=\{s\in\mathscr{D}\,|\,u_{\mathsf{b}i}(s)=\bar{u}_{i}(s)\}
. Analogously to the second case we find

\lim_{k\to\infty}\zeta^{\ell_{k}}_{i}(s)=0.

(4.5.27)

Combining (
4.5.25
)-(
4.5.27
) we conclude that
\lim_{k\to\infty}\zeta^{\ell_{k}}_{i}=0
a.e. in
\mathscr{D}
and for
1\leq i\leq\mathsf{m}
. Utilizing the dominated convergence theorem
[
RS80
, p. 24]
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\big\|\zeta^{\ell_{k}}\big\|_{\mathscr{U}}=0.

Since all subsequences contain a subsequence converging to zero, the claim follows from a standard argument.
\Box

4.5.3
Proofs of Section
4.3

Proof of Corollary
4.3.5
.
From (
4.3.15
) we infer that

{\|y^{h\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}\leq c_{y}\left({\|\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}+{\|f\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}+{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\right)

(4.5.28)

for a constant
c_{y}>0
which is independent of
\ell
. Utilizing (
A.4.6
) we have

{\|y^{h\ell}(T)\|}_{H}\leq{\|y^{h\ell}\|}_{C([0,T];H)}\leq c_{\mathscr{Y}}\,{\|y^{h\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}.

(4.5.29)

Moreover, we infer from (
2.4.1
) that

{\|y^{h\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}\leq c_{V}\,{\|y^{h\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}\leq c_{V}\,{\|y^{h\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}.

(4.5.30)

Thus, using Proposition
4.3.3
, and (
4.5.28
)-(
4.5.30
) we deduce

\displaystyle{\|p^{h\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\sigma_{2}\big({\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}+{\|y^{h\ell}(T)\|}_{H}\big)+\sigma_{1}\big({\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}+{\|y^{h\ell}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}\big)\right)

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\big({\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}+{\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}\big)+c_{2}\,{\|y^{h\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{Y}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}\left({\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}+{\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}+{\|\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}y_{\circ}\|}_{H}+{\|f\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}+{\|u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\right)

with
c_{1}=C\max(\sigma_{2},\sigma_{1})
,
c_{2}=C(\sigma_{2}c_{\mathscr{Y}}+\sigma_{1}c_{V})
and
c_{3}=\max(c_{1},c_{2}c_{\mathscr{Y}})
.
\Box

Proof of Proposition
4.3.10
.
Let the associated state variables are given by
y^{h}(u)=\hat{y}^{h}+\mathcal{S}^{h}u
and
y^{h\ell}(u)=\hat{y}^{h\ell}+\mathcal{S}^{h\ell}u
, respectively. Then, we have proved in Proposition
3.6.9
the following a-posteriori error estimate for the difference
e^{h\ell}(t;u)=y^{h}(t;u)-y^{h\ell}(t;u)\in V^{h}

{\|e^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{H}^{2}\leq\mathsf{C}_{1}^{h\ell}(t)\bigg(\mathsf{R}_{\circ}^{h\ell}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathsf{R}_{1}^{h\ell}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\bigg),

(4.5.31)

where the constants
\mathsf{C}_{1}^{h\ell}(t)
,
\mathsf{R}_{\circ}^{h\ell}
and
\mathsf{R}_{1}^{h\ell}(s)
are given by (
3.6.19a
) for almost all
s\in[0,T]
. Now we set
\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(t;u)=p^{h}(t;u)-p^{h\ell}(t;u)
for almost all
t\in[0,T]
. Then we derive from (
4.3.5
) and (
4.3.17
) for all
\varphi^{h}\in V^{h}
and
t\in[0.T]
a.e.

\displaystyle-{\langle\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(t;u),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}+a(t;\varphi^{h},\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(t;u))

\displaystyle\quad=\sigma_{1}\,{\langle{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)-y^{h}(t;u)\rangle}_{H}+{\langle p_{t}^{h\ell}(t;u),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}-a(t;\varphi^{h},p^{h\ell}(t;u))

\displaystyle\quad=-\sigma_{1}\,{\langle e^{h\ell}(t;u),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{H}-{\langle\mathsf{r}^{h\ell}(t;u),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

with the (dual) residual defined in (
4.3.27
). Choosing
\varphi=\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(t;u)\in V^{h}
we derive from (
4.1.1b
) that

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\|\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{H}^{2}+\gamma_{1}\,{\|\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{V}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq-{\langle\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(t;u),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}+a(t;\varphi^{h},\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(t;u))

\displaystyle\leq\sigma_{1}\,{\|e^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{H}{\|\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{H}+{\|\mathsf{r}^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{(V^{h})^{\prime}}{\|\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{V}.

Next we apply the Young inequality and (
2.4.1
). We get

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\|\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{H}^{2}+\gamma_{1}\,{\|\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{V}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sigma_{1}c_{V}\,{\|e^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{H}{\|\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{V}+\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\,{\|\mathsf{r}^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{(V^{h})^{\prime}}^{2}+\frac{\gamma_{1}}{4}\,{\|\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{V}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\big(\sigma_{1}^{2}c_{V}^{2}\,{\|e^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{H}^{2}+{\|\mathsf{r}^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{(V^{h})^{\prime}}^{2}\big)+\frac{\gamma_{1}}{2}\,{\|\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{V}^{2}

which implies that

-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\|\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{H}^{2}+\gamma_{1}\,{\|\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{V}^{2}\leq\frac{2}{\gamma_{1}}\big(\sigma_{1}^{2}c_{V}^{2}\,{\|e^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{H}^{2}+{\|\mathsf{r}^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{(V^{h})^{\prime}}^{2}\big).

(4.5.32)

After integration on the interval
[t,T]
with
t\in[0,T)
and using

\displaystyle\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(T;u)

\displaystyle=p^{h}(T;u)-p^{h\ell}(T;u)=\sigma_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}^{h}{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-y^{h}(T;u)\big)-\sigma_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-y^{h\ell}(T;u)\big)

(4.5.33)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}^{h}-\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}\big){y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-\sigma_{2}e^{h\ell}(T;u)

(cf. (
4.3.5b
) and (
4.3.17b
)) we find that

\displaystyle{\|\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{\|\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(T;u)\|}_{H}^{2}+\int_{t}^{T}\frac{2}{\gamma_{1}}\big(\sigma_{1}^{2}c_{V}^{2}\,{\|e^{h\ell}(s;u)\|}_{H}^{2}+{\|\mathsf{r}^{h\ell}(s;u)\|}_{(V^{h})^{\prime}}^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq 2\sigma_{2}\,{\|(\mathcal{P}^{h}-\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}){y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}^{2}+2\sigma_{2}\,{\|e^{h\ell}(T;u)\|}_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2}{\gamma_{1}}\int_{t}^{T}\sigma_{1}^{2}c_{V}^{2}\,{\|e^{h\ell}(s;u)\|}_{H}^{2}+{\|\mathsf{r}^{h\ell}(s;u)\|}_{(V^{h})^{\prime}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s.

We proceed by applying (
4.5.31
). Using the constants defined in (
4.3.26
) we conclude that

\displaystyle{\|\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{R}_{T}^{h\ell}+\mathsf{C}_{2}^{h\ell}\bigg(\mathsf{R}_{\circ}^{h\ell}+\int_{0}^{T}\mathsf{R}_{1}^{h\ell}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t}^{T}\mathsf{C}_{3}^{h\ell}(s)\bigg(\mathsf{R}_{\circ}^{h\ell}+\int_{0}^{s}\mathsf{R}_{1}^{h\ell}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau\bigg)+\frac{2}{\gamma_{1}}\,{\|\mathsf{r}^{h\ell}(s;u)\|}_{(V^{h})^{\prime}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

holds for almost all
t\in[0,T]
, where the constants
\mathsf{R}_{\circ}^{h\ell}
and
\mathsf{R}_{1}^{h\ell}(s)
are given by (
3.6.19a
). Thus, we have shown estimate (
4.3.25
). Integrating (
4.5.32
) over the interval
[0,T]
and using (
4.5.31
), (
4.5.33
) we find that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}{\|\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\,{\|\mathsf{e}^{h\ell}(T;u)\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{2}{\gamma_{1}^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}\sigma_{1}^{2}c_{V}^{2}\,{\|e^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{H}^{2}+{\|\mathsf{r}^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{(V^{h})^{\prime}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\,\bigg(\mathsf{R}_{T}^{h\ell}+\mathsf{C}_{2}^{h\ell}\Big(\mathsf{R}_{\circ}^{h\ell}+\int_{0}^{T}\mathsf{R}_{1}^{h\ell}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\Big)\bigg)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\int_{0}^{T}\mathsf{C}_{3}^{h\ell}(s)\bigg(\mathsf{R}_{\circ}^{h\ell}+\int_{0}^{s}\mathsf{R}_{1}^{h\ell}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau\bigg)+\frac{2}{\gamma_{1}}\,{\|\mathsf{r}^{h\ell}(t;u)\|}_{(V^{h})^{\prime}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

which gives estimate (
4.3.28
).
\Box

4.5.4
Proofs of Section
4.4

Proof of Theorem
4.4.4
.

1)

Boundedness of the minimizing sequence: Let

\displaystyle\bar{J}=\inf\big\{J(y^{\ell},u)\,\big|\,x=(\mathrm{y}^{\ell},y,p,\psi,\lambda,u)\in{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}\big\}.

Since
J
is bounded from below by
0
, we have
\bar{J}\geq 0
. Consequently, there exists a minimizing sequence
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
with
x_{n}=(\mathrm{y}^{\ell}_{n},y_{n},p_{n},\psi_{n},\lambda_{n},u_{n})
and

\lim_{n\to\infty}J(x_{n})=\bar{J}.

(4.5.34)

Moreover,
J
is radially unbounded with respect to the control variabe so that
\{u_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is bounded in
{\mathscr{U}}
. We deduce from Remark
3.3.8
-1) that
\mathrm{y}^{\ell}
is bounded in
H^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{\ell})
. The bound depends on
\ell
, but
\ell
is fixed here. It follows from (
3.2.5
) and Lemma
4.1.22
-1) that
\{y_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
y_{n}=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u_{n}
, and
\{p_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
p_{n}=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}u_{n}
, are bounded sequences in
W(0,T)
. Clearly,
\{\psi_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded in
\mathbb{X}^{\ell}
. Furthermore, it follows from

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{R}(y_{n},p_{n})\|}_{\mathscr{L}(X)}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|\psi\|_{X}=1}{\|\mathcal{R}(y_{n},p_{n})\psi\|}_{X}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|\psi\|_{X}=1}\bigg\|\int_{0}^{T}{\langle y_{n}(t),\psi\rangle}_{X}\,y_{n}(t)+{\langle p_{n}(t),\psi\rangle}_{X}\,p_{n}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\bigg\|_{X}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\|\psi\|_{X}=1}\int_{0}^{T}{\|y_{n}(t)\|}_{X}^{2}{\|\psi\|}_{X}+{\|p_{n}(t)\|}_{X}^{2}{\|\psi\|}_{X}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq{\|y_{n}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}+{\|p_{n}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left({\|y_{\circ}\|}_{H}+{\|f\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})}+{\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}+{\|{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}+{\|u_{n}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\right)

that the sequence
\{\mathcal{R}(y_{n},p_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded in
\mathscr{L}(X)
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\lambda_{i,n}=\lambda_{i,n}\,{\|\psi_{i,n}\|}_{X}={\|\lambda_{i,n}\psi_{i,n}\|}_{X}={\|\mathcal{R}(y_{n},p_{n})\psi_{i,n}\|}_{X}\leq C\quad\text{for all }n\in\mathbb{N}

implies that
\{\lambda_{i,n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded in
\mathbb{R}
as well for
i=1,\ldots,\ell
. Therefore, we have proved that the sequence
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is bounded in the Hilbert space
{\mathscr{X}}
. Since
{\mathscr{X}}
is a Hilbert space, there exist a subsequence of
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
which we again denote by
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and an element
\bar{x}=(\bar{y}^{\ell},\bar{y},\bar{p},\bar{\psi},\bar{\lambda},\bar{u})\in{\mathscr{X}}
satisfying

x_{n}\rightharpoonup\bar{x}\text{ in }{\mathscr{X}}\text{ for }n\to\infty\text{ in }\mathscr{X}.

(4.5.35)

2)

Feasibility of the limit point
\bar{x}
: Since
{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is weakly closed, we have
\bar{u}\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
. From
y_{n}\rightharpoonup\bar{y}
in
W(0,T)
and
u_{n}\rightharpoonup\bar{u}
in
{\mathscr{U}}
for
n\to\infty
it follows that

\displaystyle{\langle y_{n,t}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

\displaystyle\to{\langle\bar{y}_{t}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V},\quad a(t;y_{n}(t),\varphi)\to a(t;\bar{y}(t),\varphi),

\displaystyle{\langle(\mathcal{B}u_{n})(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

\displaystyle\to{\langle(\mathcal{B}\bar{u})(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

for every
\varphi\in V
a.e. in
[0,T]
. Hence,

\displaystyle e_{3}(\bar{x})=0\text{ in }{\mathscr{P}}_{3}^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad e_{4}(\bar{x})=0\text{ in }{\mathscr{P}}_{4}.

Using
y_{n}\rightharpoonup\bar{y}
and
p_{n}\rightharpoonup\bar{p}
in
W(0,T)
for
n\to\infty
implies that

\displaystyle{\langle p_{n,t}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

\displaystyle\to{\langle\bar{p}_{t}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V},\quad a(t;\varphi,p_{n}(t))\to a(t;\varphi,\bar{p}(t)),

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}\,{\langle y_{n,t}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle\to\sigma_{1}\,{\langle\bar{y}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

for every
\varphi\in V
a.e. in
[0,T]
. We conclude that

\displaystyle e_{5}(\bar{x})=0\text{ in }{\mathscr{P}}_{5}^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad e_{6}(\bar{x})=0\text{ in }{\mathscr{P}}_{6}.

Next we argue that
\bar{\lambda}_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq\ell
, is an eigenvalue of
\mathcal{R}(\bar{y},\bar{p})
with eigenvector
\bar{\psi}_{i}
. By Assumption
4.4.2
we have

(y_{n},p_{n})\to(\bar{y},\bar{p})\quad\text{in }L^{2}(0,T;V)\times L^{2}(0,T;V)\text{ for }k\to\infty.

(4.5.36)

Therefore, it follows from (
4.5.35
) and (
4.5.36
) that

\displaystyle\lambda_{i,n}\,{\langle\psi_{i,n},\psi\rangle}_{X}

\displaystyle={\langle\mathcal{R}(y_{n},p_{n})\psi_{i,n},\psi\rangle}_{X}

(4.5.37)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}{\langle y_{n}(t),\psi_{i,n}\rangle}_{X}\,{\langle y_{n}(t),\psi\rangle}_{X}+{\langle p_{n}(t),\psi_{i,n}\rangle}_{X}\,{\langle p_{n}(t),\psi\rangle}_{X}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\int_{0}^{T}{\langle\bar{y}(t),\bar{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,{\langle\bar{y}(t),\psi\rangle}_{X}+{\langle\bar{p}(t),\bar{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,{\langle\bar{p}(t),\psi\rangle}_{X}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle={\langle\mathcal{R}(\bar{y},\bar{p})\bar{\psi}_{i},\psi\rangle}_{X}\quad\text{for all }\psi\in X.

Since
\{\lambda_{n}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence in
\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
, we also have

\lambda^{n}\to\bar{\lambda}\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\text{ for }k\to\infty.

(4.5.38)

Thus, passing to the limit on the left in (
4.5.37
) yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\bar{y},\bar{p})\bar{\psi}_{i}=\bar{\lambda}_{i}\bar{\psi}_{i}\quad\text{for }1\leq i\leq\ell.

Consequently,
(\bar{\psi}_{i},\bar{\lambda}_{i})
,
i=1,\ldots,\ell
, is an eigenvector-eigenvalue pair for the operator
\mathcal{R}(\bar{y},\bar{p})
so that we have

\displaystyle e_{7}(\bar{x})=0\text{ in }{\mathscr{P}}_{7}.

Furthermore, by (
4.5.35
) we find that

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}_{i}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\lambda_{i,n}=\lim_{n\to\infty}{\langle\lambda_{i,n}\psi_{i,n},\psi_{i,n}\rangle}_{X}=\lim_{k\to\infty}{\langle\mathcal{R}(y_{n},p_{n})\psi_{i,n},\psi_{i,n}\rangle}_{X}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\big|{\langle y_{n},\psi_{i,n}\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}+\big|{\langle p_{n},\psi_{i,n}\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\big|{\langle\bar{y},\bar{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}+\big|{\langle\bar{p},\bar{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{X}\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t={\langle\mathcal{R}(\bar{y},\bar{p})\bar{\psi}_{i},\bar{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{X}={\langle\bar{\lambda}_{i}\bar{\psi}_{i},\bar{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{X}=\bar{\lambda}_{i}\,{\|\bar{\psi}_{i}\|}_{X}^{2}

for
1\leq i\leq\ell
. By assumption, we have
\bar{\lambda}_{i}>0
for
1\leq i\leq\ell
. Thus, we have
\|\bar{\psi}_{i}\|_{X}=1
and

\displaystyle e_{8}(\bar{x})=0\text{ in }{\mathscr{P}}_{8}.

Since the norm is weakly lower semicontinuous, we deduce

\displaystyle 1={\|\bar{\psi}_{i}\|}_{X}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}{\|\psi_{i,n}\|}_{X}=1\quad\text{for }1\leq i\leq\ell

which implies that

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\|\psi_{i,n}\|}_{X}={\|\bar{\psi}_{i}\|}_{X}\quad\text{for }1\leq i\leq\ell.

(4.5.39)

Thus,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}{\|\psi_{i,n}-\bar{\psi}_{i}\|}_{X}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}{\langle\psi_{i,n}-\bar{\psi}_{i},\psi_{i,n}-\bar{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{X}

(4.5.40)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}{\langle\psi_{i,n}-\bar{\psi}_{i},\psi_{i,n}\rangle}_{X}+\lim_{n\to\infty}{\langle\bar{\psi}_{i}-\psi_{i,n},\bar{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{X}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}{\|\psi_{i,n}\|}_{X}^{2}-\lim_{n\to\infty}{\langle\bar{\psi}_{i},\psi_{i,n}\rangle}_{X}+\lim_{k\to\infty}{\langle\bar{\psi}_{i}-\psi_{i,n},\bar{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{X}

\displaystyle={\|\bar{\psi}_{i}\|}_{X}^{2}-{\|\bar{\psi}_{i}\|}_{X}^{2}+0=0

because of (
4.5.39
) and (
4.5.35
). This also implies that

{\langle\bar{\psi}_{i},\bar{\psi}_{j}\rangle}_{X}=\delta_{ij}\quad\text{for }1\leq i,j\leq\ell.

(4.5.41)

Moreover, we have
\psi_{i,n}\to\bar{\psi}
in
V
for
1\leq i\leq\ell
: In fact, for the choice
X=V
we have already proved this claim. Let u consider the case
X=H
. We define the four functions

\displaystyle r_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{i,n}}\,{\langle y_{n}(\cdot),\psi_{i^{n}}\rangle}_{H}\in L^{2}(0,T),

\displaystyle\bar{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\bar{\lambda}_{i}}\,{\langle\bar{y}(\cdot),\bar{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{H}\in L^{2}(0,T),

\displaystyle s_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{i,n}}\,{\langle p_{n}(\cdot),\psi_{i^{n}}\rangle}_{H}\in L^{2}(0,T),

\displaystyle\bar{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\bar{\lambda}_{i}}\,{\langle\bar{p}(\cdot),\bar{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{H}\in L^{2}(0,T).

It follows from (
4.5.35
) and (
4.5.36
) that

\lim_{n\to\infty}r_{n}=\bar{r}\text{ in }L^{2}(0,T)\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}s_{n}=\bar{s}\text{ in }L^{2}(0,T).

(4.5.42)

Therefore, we derive from (
4.5.36
) and (
4.5.40
) and (
4.5.42
)

\displaystyle{\|\psi_{i,n}-\bar{\psi}_{i}\|}_{V}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\frac{1}{\lambda_{i,n}}\,\mathcal{R}(y_{n},p_{n})\psi_{i,n}-\frac{1}{\bar{\lambda}_{i}}\,\mathcal{R}(\bar{y},\bar{p})\bar{\psi}_{i}\bigg\|_{V}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\frac{1}{\lambda_{i,n}}\int_{0}^{T}{\langle y_{n}(t),\psi_{i,n}\rangle}_{X}\,y_{n}(t)+{\langle p_{n}(t),\psi_{i,n}\rangle}_{X}\,p_{n}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{\bar{\lambda}_{i}}\int_{0}^{T}{\langle\bar{y}(t),\bar{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\bar{y}(t)+{\langle\bar{p}(t),\bar{\psi}_{i}\rangle}_{X}\,\bar{p}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\bigg\|_{V}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\big\|r_{n}(t)\,y_{n}(t)+s_{n}\,p_{n}(t)-\bar{r}(t)\,\bar{y}(t)-\bar{s}(t)\,\bar{p}(t)\big\|_{V}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\big|r_{n}(t)-\bar{r}(t)\big|{\|y_{n}(t)\|}_{V}+|\bar{r}(t)|{\|y_{n}(t)-\bar{y}(t)\|}_{V}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{T}\big|s_{n}-\bar{s}(t)\big|{\|p_{n}(t)\|}_{V}+|\bar{s}(t)|{\|p_{n}(t)-\bar{p}(t)\|}_{V}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq{\|r_{n}-\bar{r}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T)}{\|y_{n}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T,V)}+{\|\bar{r}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T)}\,{\|y_{n}-\bar{y}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}

\displaystyle\quad+{\|s^{n_{k}}-\bar{s}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T)}{\|p_{n}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T,V)}+{\|\bar{s}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T)}\,{\|p_{n}-\bar{p}\|}_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0

for
1\leq i\leq\ell
. Using the weak convergence of the sequence
\{\mathrm{y}_{n}^{\ell}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
H^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{\ell})
and the strong convergence of
\{\phi_{i,n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
1\leq i\leq\ell
, we derive that

\displaystyle e_{1}(\bar{x})=0\text{ in }{\mathscr{P}}_{1}^{\prime},\quad e_{2}(\bar{x})=0\text{ in }{\mathscr{P}}_{2}.

holds. Summarizing we have proved that
\bar{u}\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
and
e(\bar{x})=0
in
{\mathscr{P}}^{\prime}
is true which gives
\bar{x}\in{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
.

3)

Optimality of
\bar{z}
: Since the cost functional is weakly lower semicontinuous, we have

\displaystyle 0\geq\bar{J}=\inf\big\{J(y^{\ell},u)\,\big|\,x\in{{\mathscr{Z}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}\big\}=\lim_{n\to\infty}J(x_{n})=\liminf_{n\to\infty}J(x_{n})\geq J(\bar{x})

which gives the claim.
\Box

Chapter 5
Advanced Topics in POD Suboptimal Control

5.1
Optimal control of nonlinear evolution problems

It has been shown in the previous chapters that POD can be easily and efficiently applied to optimal control problems with linear state equations. This linearity made it straightforward to define a POD Galerkin scheme in (
3.3.2
) which could be evaluated exclusively in the low-order subspace
V^{\ell}
by the system of ordinary differential equations (
3.3.7
) for the basis representation. In this chapter, we will elaborate on the difficulties that arise with a nonlinear state equation and how these can be overcome. For the sake of simplicity, we work with the particular semilinear example below, even though the presented strategies extend to the general case.

5.1.1
State equation

Suppose that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a bounded Lipschitz domain with boundary
\Gamma=\partial\Omega
and
T>0
is a final time. As before, we denote the space-time cylinder by
Q=(0,T)\times\Omega
and the boundary-time cylinder by
\Sigma=(0,T)\times\Gamma
. We consider the semilinear heat equation

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} y_{t}(t,{\bm{x}})-c\Delta y(t,{\bm{x}})+y(t,x)^{3}&=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}u_{i}(t)\chi_{i}({\bm{x}}),&&(t,{\bm{x}})\in Q,\\
c\frac{\partial y}{\partial{\bm{n}}}(t,{\bm{s}})&=0,&&(t,{\bm{s}})\in\Sigma,\\
y(0,{\bm{x}})&=y_{\circ}({\bm{x}}),&&x\in\Omega.\end{aligned}

(5.1.1)

In contrast to the general example in Section
1.2
, there is now a constant diffusivity
c>0
and a nonlinear cubic term that is evaluated at the state value
y(t,{\bm{x}})
. Because there are no nonlinear evaluations of
y
-derivatives, the above system is referred to as a
semilinear
equation. All remaining terms are identical to Section
1.2
, to which we refer for further details. We use the typical Gelfand triple given by the spaces
V=H^{1}(\Omega)
,
H=L^{2}(\Omega)
and define the solution space
{\mathscr{Y}}=W(0,T)\cap L^{\infty}(\overline{Q})
, where the space
W(0,T)=H^{1}(0,T;V^{\prime})\cap L^{2}(0,T;V)
is introduced in Section
A.4
. Furthermore, the control space is given by
{\mathscr{U}}=L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}})
, with an inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
that is still left to be specified. Let us assume that it holds
\chi_{1},...,\chi_{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
and
y_{\circ}\in C(\overline{\Omega})
.

These conditions allow us to define the symmetric bilinear form
a:V\times V\to\mathbb{R}
as

\displaystyle a(\varphi,\psi)=c\int_{\Omega}\nabla\varphi(t,{\bm{x}})\cdot\nabla\psi({\bm{x}})~\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\qquad\forall\varphi,\psi\in V.

(5.1.2a)

For all
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
and almost all
t\in(0,T)
, we also define the time-dependent linear form
(\mathcal{B}u)(t):V\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle{\langle(\mathcal{B}u)(t),\psi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}u_{i}(t)\int_{\Omega}\xi_{i}({\bm{x}})\psi({\bm{x}})~\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\quad\text{for all }\psi\in V.

(5.1.2b)

Finally, let us denote for
z:Q\to\mathbb{R}
the function
\mathcal{N}(z):Q\to\mathbb{R}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(z)(t,{\bm{x}})=z(t,{\bm{x}})^{3}\quad\text{f.a.a. }{\bm{x}}\in Q.

(5.1.2c)

Similar to the function
y(t)
standing for
y(t,\cdot):\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
for
t\in(0,T)
, we also abbreviate the function
\mathcal{N}(y(t)):=\mathcal{N}(y(t,\cdot)):\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 5.1.1
.

A function
y\in{\mathscr{Y}}
is called a weak solution of (
5.1.1
) if it satisfies for all
\varphi\in V
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\langle y(t),\varphi\rangle_{H}+a(y(t),\varphi)+\langle\mathcal{N}(y(t)),\varphi\rangle_{H}&=\langle(\mathcal{B}u)(t),\varphi\rangle_{V^{\prime},V}&&\text{f.a.a. }t\in(0,T),\\
\langle y(0),\varphi\rangle_{H}&=\langle y_{\circ},\varphi\rangle_{H}.&&\end{aligned}

(5.1.3)

Notice that the above definition is well-defined since it follows from
y(t)\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
for almost all
t\in(0,T)
that also
\mathcal{N}(y(t))\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, which embeds into
L^{2}(\Omega)
.

Theorem 5.1.2
.

For every
u\in L^{r}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}})
with
r>d/2+1
, the weak state equation (
5.1.3
) admits a unique solution
y\in{\mathscr{Y}}\cap C(\overline{Q})
which satisfies the energy estimate

\displaystyle{\|y\|}_{W(0,T)}+{\|y\|}_{C(\overline{Q})}\leq c_{\infty}\big(\|u\|_{L^{r}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}})}+{\|y_{\circ}\|}_{C(\overline{Q})}\big)

(5.1.4)

with a constant
c_{\infty}>0
that does not depend on
u
or
y_{\circ}
.

Proof.

Let us define the inhomogeneity function

\displaystyle f:Q\to\mathbb{R},\quad f(t,{\bm{x}})=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}u_{i}(t)\chi_{i}({\bm{x}}).

We can utilize the fact that
\chi_{i}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
for
i=1,...,{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}
and easily verify that
f\in L^{r}(Q)
with

\displaystyle{\|f\|}_{L^{r}(Q)}\leq c\,{\|u\|}_{L^{r}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}})}

with a constant
c>0
that does not depend on
u
. Inserting these two properties into
[
Trö10
, Theorem 5.5]
gives the claim.
\Box

From Theorem
5.1.2
, we get a well-defined solution operator
\mathcal{S}:{\mathscr{U}}\to{\mathscr{Y}}\cap C(\overline{\Omega})
which is defined on
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{S}):=L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}})
.

5.1.2
Semidiscretization by FE and POD

As it was extensively studied in Section
3.4
for the linear problem class, the standard way to proceed is to introduce a space discretization, for which we choose linear Finite Elements (FE). Let therefore

\displaystyle V^{h}=\text{span }\{\varphi_{1}^{h},\ldots,\varphi_{m}^{h}\}\subset V

denote the ansatz space. The semidiscrete FE Galerkin discretization of (
5.1.3
) is given by a function
y^{h}\in H^{1}(0,T;V^{h})\cap L^{\infty}(\overline{Q})
which solves for all
\varphi^{h}\in V^{h}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle y^{h}(t),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{H}+a(y^{h}(t),\varphi^{h})+{\langle\mathcal{N}(y^{h}(t)),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{H}&={\langle(\mathcal{B}u)(t),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\quad\text{f.a.a. }t\in(0,T),\\
{\langle y(0),\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{H}&={\langle y_{\circ},\varphi^{h}\rangle}_{H}.\end{aligned}

(5.1.5)

Along the lines of Section
3.6.3
, we can show that there exists an equivalent algebraic ODE system to (
5.1.5
). If
\mathrm{y}^{h}:(0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is the coordinate function to
y^{h}
, it satisfies (cf. (
3.6.13
))

\displaystyle{\bm{M}}^{h}\dot{\mathrm{y}}^{h}(t)+{\bm{A}}^{h}\mathrm{y}^{h}(t)+\mathrm{n}^{h}\big(\mathrm{y}^{h}(t)\big)

\displaystyle={\bm{B}}^{h}u(t)\quad\text{for }t\in(0,T],

(5.1.6)

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}^{h}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{y}_{\circ}^{h}.

Here, the matrices
{\bm{M}}^{h}
and
{\bm{A}}^{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
are given by

\displaystyle{\bm{M}}^{h}_{ij}=\int_{\Omega}\varphi_{i}^{h}({\bm{x}})\varphi_{j}^{h}({\bm{x}})~\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}},\quad{\bm{A}}_{ij}^{h}=a(\varphi^{h}_{i},\varphi^{h}_{j}),\quad\text{for all }i,j=1,...,m.

The matrix
{\bm{B}}^{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}}
is given by the elements

\displaystyle{\bm{B}}^{h}_{ij}=\int_{\Omega}\xi_{j}({\bm{x}})\varphi^{h}_{i}({\bm{x}})~\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\quad\text{for all }i=1,\ldots,m,~j=1,\ldots,{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}.

The initial condition is the coordinate representation of the
H
-orthogonal projection
y_{\circ}^{h}=\mathcal{P}^{h}y_{\circ}
onto
V^{h}
. Finally, the nonlinearity
\mathrm{n}^{h}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
reads

\displaystyle\mathrm{n}^{h}(\mathrm{v})=\bigg(\int_{\Omega}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathrm{v}_{j}\varphi_{j}^{h}({\bm{x}})\Big)^{3}\varphi_{i}^{h}({\bm{x}})~\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\bigg)_{1\leq i\leq m}

Since a correct evaluation of this function would force us to leave the linear FE space, we make the nodal approximation

\displaystyle\mathrm{n}^{h}(\mathrm{v})\approx\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{v}_{j}^{3}\varphi_{j}^{h}({\bm{x}})\varphi_{i}^{h}({\bm{x}})~\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\bigg)_{1\leq i\leq m}={\bm{M}}^{h}\mathrm{N}^{h}(\mathrm{v}),

where we have defined the coordinate nonlinearity
\mathrm{N}^{h}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
with
\mathrm{N}^{h}_{i}(\mathrm{v})=\mathrm{v}_{i}^{3}
for
1\leq i\leq m
. In replacement of (
5.1.6
), we now get the system

\displaystyle{\bm{M}}^{h}\dot{\mathrm{y}}^{h}(t)+{\bm{A}}^{h}\mathrm{y}^{h}(t)+{\bm{M}}^{h}\mathrm{N}^{h}(y^{h}(t))

\displaystyle={\bm{B}}^{h}u(t)\quad\text{for }t\in(0,T],

(5.1.7)

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}^{h}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{y}_{\circ}^{h}.

In a next step, let us suppose that we have computed a POD subspace

\displaystyle V^{h\ell}=\text{span }\{\varphi^{h\ell}_{1},\ldots,\varphi^{h\ell}_{\ell}\}\subset V^{h}\subset V

of dimension
\ell\ll m
. The POD Galerkin discretization of (
5.1.5
) is given by a function
y^{h\ell}\in H^{1}(0,T;V^{h\ell})\cap L^{\infty}(Q)
which solves for all
\varphi^{h\ell}\in V^{h\ell}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle y^{h\ell}(t),\varphi^{h\ell}\rangle}_{H}+&a(y^{h\ell}(t),\varphi^{h\ell})+{\langle\mathcal{N}(y^{h\ell}(t)),\varphi^{h\ell}\rangle}_{H}&={\langle(\mathcal{B}u)(t),\varphi^{h\ell}\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\qquad\text{f.a.a. }t\in(0,T),\\
{\langle y(0),\varphi^{h\ell}\rangle}_{H}&=\langle y_{\circ},\varphi^{h\ell}\rangle_{H}.\end{aligned}

(5.1.8)

With the same nodal appoximation for the nonlinearity, we get the ODE system for the POD coordinate solution
\mathrm{y}^{\ell}:(0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{\ell}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {\bm{M}}^{h\ell}\dot{\mathrm{y}}^{h\ell}(t)+{\bm{A}}^{h\ell}\mathrm{y}^{h\ell}(t)+({\bm{\Psi}}^{h\ell})^{\top}\mathrm{N}^{h}\big({\bm{\Psi}}^{h\ell}\mathrm{y}^{\ell}(t)\big)&={\bm{B}}^{h\ell}u(t)\quad&\text{for }t\in(0,T],\\
{\bm{M}}^{h\ell}\mathrm{y}^{h\ell}(0)&=\mathrm{y}_{\circ}^{h\ell}.\end{aligned}

(5.1.9)

The matrix
{\bm{\Psi}}^{h\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times\ell}
holds the coordinates of the POD basis vectors such that

\displaystyle\varphi_{j}^{h\ell}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\bm{\Psi}}^{h\ell}_{ij}\varphi^{h}_{i},\qquad\text{for }j=1,\ldots,\ell.

The matrices
{\bm{M}}^{h\ell}
and
{\bm{A}}^{h\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell\times\ell}
indicate the mass and stiffness matrices of the POD system and are given by

\displaystyle{\bm{M}}^{h\ell}=\Big(\int_{\Omega}\varphi^{h\ell}_{i}({\bm{x}})\varphi^{h\ell}_{j}({\bm{x}})~\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\Big)_{1\leq i,j\leq\ell}=({\bm{\Psi}}^{h\ell})^{\top}{\bm{M}}^{h}{\bm{\Psi}}^{h\ell},~{\bm{A}}^{h\ell}=\Big(a(\varphi^{h\ell}_{i},\varphi^{h\ell}_{j})\Big)_{1\leq i,j\leq\ell}=({\bm{\Psi}}^{h\ell})^{\top}{\bm{A}}^{h}{\bm{\Psi}}^{h\ell}.

The same holds true for the control matrix
{\bm{B}}^{h\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell\times{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}}
, which satisfies

\displaystyle{\bm{B}}^{h\ell}=\Big(\int_{\Omega}\xi_{j}({\bm{x}})\varphi_{j}^{h\ell}({\bm{x}})~\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\Big)_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq\ell\\
1\leq j\leq{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}\end{subarray}}=({\bm{\Psi}}^{h\ell})^{\top}{\bm{B}}^{h}.

The initial vector
\mathrm{y}^{h\ell}_{\circ}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
is given by the coordinate representatin of the
H
-orthogonal projection
y_{\circ}^{h\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{h\ell}y_{\circ}^{h}
onto
V^{h\ell}
. It can be easily shown that it is implicitly given be the linear system
{\bm{M}}^{h\ell}\mathrm{y}_{\circ}^{h\ell}=({\bm{\Psi}}^{h\ell})^{\top}{\bm{M}}^{h}\mathrm{y}_{\circ}^{h}
.

The efficiency-related problem in (
5.1.9
) concerning the nonlinearity was already mentioned in Section
3.6.5
. It lies in the fact that it enforces us to enhance the low-dimensional vector
\mathrm{y}^{\ell}(t)
to the FE size
m
by multiplication with
{\bm{\Psi}}^{h\ell}
, perform a full-order evaluation of the nonlinearity
\mathrm{N}
and then re-compress the result to
\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
by multiplication with
({\bm{\Psi}}^{h\ell})^{\top}
. As a workaround, both the Empirical Interpolation method (EIM, Algorithm
3.6.5
and the Discrete Empirical Interpolation Method (DEIM, Algorithm
3.6.5
) are available. After the computation of a matrix
{\bm{\Phi}}^{\mathsf{p}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times\mathsf{p}}
and a projection matrix
{\bm{P}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times\mathsf{p}}
, we have the interpolated nonlinearity

\displaystyle\mathrm{N}^{\mathsf{p}\ell}:\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{p}},\quad\mathrm{N}^{\mathsf{p}\ell}(\mathrm{y}^{h\ell})=({\bm{P}}^{\top}{\bm{\Phi}}^{\mathsf{p}})^{-1}{\bm{P}}^{\top}\mathrm{N}^{h}({\bm{\Psi}}^{h\ell}\mathrm{y}^{h\ell}),

which was indicated by the vector
\mathrm{c}
in Section
3.6.5
. Since multiplication with the projection matrix
{\bm{P}}^{\top}
only selects
\mathsf{p}
components of a vector in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and the nonlinearity
\mathrm{N}^{h}
works componentwise, the above function satisfies

\displaystyle\mathrm{N}^{\mathsf{p}\ell}(\mathrm{y}^{h\ell})=({\bm{P}}^{\top}{\bm{\Phi}}^{\mathsf{p}})^{-1}\mathrm{N}^{\mathsf{p}}({\bm{P}}^{\top}{\bm{\Psi}}^{h\ell}\mathrm{y}^{h\ell}),

where the nonlinearity
\mathrm{N}^{\mathsf{p}}:\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{p}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{p}}
is given by
\mathrm{N}_{i}^{\ell}\mathrm{v}=\mathrm{v}_{i}^{3}
for
i=1,\ldots,p
. Replacing the system (
5.1.9
) with the inclusion of this interpolated nonlinearity, we end up with

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {\bm{M}}^{h\ell}\dot{\mathrm{y}}^{h\ell}(t)+{\bm{A}}^{h\ell}\mathrm{y}^{h\ell}(t)+({\bm{\Psi}}^{h\ell})^{\top}{\bm{M}}^{h\ell}{\bm{\Phi}}^{\mathsf{p}}({\bm{P}}^{\top}{\bm{\Phi}}^{\mathsf{p}})^{-1}\mathrm{N}^{\mathsf{p}}\big({\bm{P}}^{\top}{\bm{\Psi}}^{h\ell}\mathrm{y}^{\ell}(t)\big)&={\bm{B}}^{h\ell}u(t),\\
{\bm{M}}^{h\ell}\mathrm{y}^{h\ell}(0)&=\mathrm{y}_{\circ}^{h\ell}.\end{aligned}

(5.1.10)

for
t\in(0,T]
. Even though this may look more complex than the original version (
5.1.9
), the matrices
({\bm{\Psi}}^{h\ell})^{\top}{\bm{M}}^{h\ell}{\bm{\Phi}}^{\mathsf{p}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell\times\mathsf{p}}
,
{\bm{P}}^{\top}{\bm{\Phi}}^{\mathsf{p}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{p}\times\mathsf{p}}
and
{\bm{P}}^{\top}{\bm{\Psi}}^{h\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{p}\times\ell}
are of reduced dimension and can be pre-computed to save on efficiency. The remaining nonlinearity
\mathrm{N}^{\mathsf{p}}
only operates in the (D)EIM dimension
\mathsf{p}\ll m
, so the complexity is massively reduced compared to (
5.1.9
).

5.1.3
Cost function and optimal control problem

For the cost function, we choose a desired final-time state
{y^{\mathsf{d}}}\in C(\overline{Q})
and set

\displaystyle J:{\mathscr{Y}}\times{\mathscr{U}}\to\mathbb{R},\quad J(y,u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\big|y(T,{\bm{x}})-{y^{\mathsf{d}}}({\bm{x}})\big|^{2}~\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}+\frac{\sigma}{2}\lVert u\rVert_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}.

As usual, the reduced cost function is defined as
\hat{J}(u)=J(\mathcal{S}(u),u)
for all
u\in L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}})
, cf. Theorem
5.1.2
. On the control space
{\mathscr{U}}
, we impose the partial order relation
u\leq\tilde{u}
as in Remark
4.1.2
. For two bilateral constraints given by bounding variables
u_{\mathsf{a}},u_{\mathsf{b}}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}})
with
u_{\mathsf{a}}\leq u_{\mathsf{b}}
, the admissible set is given as in Definition
4.1.6
by

\displaystyle{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}=\big\{u\in{\mathscr{U}}~\big|~u_{\mathsf{a}}\leq u\leq u_{\mathsf{b}}\big\}

Notice that
{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is a bounded subset of
L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}})
. Therefore, the solution operator
\mathcal{S}
and with it the reduced cost function
\hat{J}
are well-defined on
{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
. Within this admissible set, the continuous optimal control problem is now given by

\displaystyle\min\limits_{u\in{\mathscr{U}}}\quad\hat{J}(u)\quad\text{s.t.}\quad u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

(5.1.11)

Notice that unlike the main linear-quadratic problem (

^
P

) considered in Chapter
4
, this problem is not convex because of the nonlinearity of the solution operator
\mathcal{S}
contained in the function
\hat{J}
. Nevertheless, a result about solvability of this problem can be derived.

Theorem 5.1.3
.

The constrained optimal control problem (
5.1.11
) admits at least one globally optimal solution
\bar{u}\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
.

Proof.

We follow along the lines of
[
Trö10
, Theorem 5.7]
and take
r>d/2+1
. Since
{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is bounded in
L^{r}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}})
, (
5.1.4
) gives us the existence of a constant
M>0
with

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{S}(u)\|}_{C(\overline{Q})}\leq M\quad\text{for all }u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

Next, we take a minimizing sequence
\{u_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
with

\displaystyle\hat{J}(u_{n})\to J^{*}:=\inf\limits_{u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}}\hat{J}(u)\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

This is possible since the cost function
\hat{J}
is nonnegative. We also define
y_{n}:=\mathcal{S}(u_{n})
. Recall that
{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is a closed, convex, and bounded subset of the reflexive Banach space
L^{r}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}})
. As such, it is weakly sequentially compact, cf.
[
Trö10
, Theorem 2.11]
. Therefore we have a weakly convergent subsequent of
\{u_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, which without loss of generality we write again as
\{u_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. Let the weak limit be denoted by
\bar{u}\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
. In a next step, we write the nonlinearity and the control input as

\displaystyle z_{n}:Q

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle z_{n}(t,{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=y(t,{\bm{x}})^{3}

\displaystyle\text{for }n\in\mathbb{N},

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}:L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}})

\displaystyle\to L^{\infty}(Q),

\displaystyle(\mathcal{B}u)(t,{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}}u_{i}(t)\chi_{i}({\bm{x}})

With it, the state equation (
5.1.1
) for the sequence states can be equivalently formulated as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} y^{n}_{t}(t,{\bm{x}})-c\Delta y^{n}(t,{\bm{x}})&=-z_{n}(t,{\bm{x}})+(\mathcal{B}u_{n})(t,{\bm{x}}),&&(t,{\bm{x}})\in Q,\\
c\,\frac{\partial y^{n}}{\partial{\bm{n}}}(t,{\bm{s}})&=0,&&(t,{\bm{s}})\in\Sigma,\\
y^{n}(0,{\bm{x}})&=y_{\circ}({\bm{x}}),&&x\in\Omega.\end{aligned}

(5.1.12)

This linear equation possesses a well-defined, affine linear and continuous solution operator mapping from
L^{2}(Q)\to W(0,T)
, cf. Corollary
3.2.7
. As such, this solution operator is also weakly continuous (cf.
[
Trö10
, p.45]
). The same reasoning applies to
\mathcal{B}
, which gives
\mathcal{B}u_{n}\to\mathcal{B}\bar{u}
in
L^{\infty}(Q)
and therefore also in
L^{2}(Q)
. Furthermore, the sequence
\{z_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset L^{\infty}(Q)
is bounded in
L^{\infty}(Q)
and theefore has a weakly convergent subsequence. Without loss of generality, we again write
z_{n}\rightharpoonup\bar{z}\in L^{\infty}(Q)\hookrightarrow L^{2}(Q)
. Together, this gives us

\displaystyle-z_{n}+\mathcal{B}u_{n}\rightharpoonup-\bar{z}+\mathcal{B}\bar{u}\quad\text{in }L^{2}(Q)\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

With the already mentioned weak continuity of the solution operator of (
5.1.12
), this gives us the weak convergence of
y_{n}
in
W(0,T)
to an element
\bar{y}\in W(0,T)
. The rest of the proof is identical to that of
[
Trö10
, Theorem 5.7]
and we will only outline the last steps: The fact that
y_{\circ}\in C(\overline{\Omega})
allows to conclude that
y_{n}
even convergences strongly in
W(0,T)
to
\bar{y}
. Further, we find by local Lipschitz continuity of the cubic nonlinearity that it holds
y_{n}(\cdot)^{3}\to\bar{y}(\cdot)^{3}
in
L^{\infty}(Q)
. This can be used to show
\bar{y}
is the solution of the state equation (
5.1.3
) for
u=\bar{u}
. Finally, this allows to conclude:

\displaystyle J^{*}=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\hat{J}(u^{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}\big|y^{n}(T,{\bm{x}})-{y^{\mathsf{d}}}({\bm{x}})\big|^{2}~\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}+\sigma\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|u^{n}\|_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\Big(\int_{\Omega}\big|\bar{y}(T,{\bm{x}})-{y^{\mathsf{d}}}({\bm{x}})\big|^{2}~\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}+\sigma\|\bar{u}\|_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}\Big)=\hat{J}(\bar{u}).

Notice that the strong convergence
y^{n}\to\bar{y}
in
W(0,T)
extends to strong convergence
y^{n}(T)\to\bar{y}(T)
in
H=L^{2}(\Omega)
due to the embedding
W(0,T)\hookrightarrow C([0,T];H)
, such that the first term converges exactly. Furthermore, the weak convergence
u^{n}\rightharpoonup\bar{u}
together with the lower semicontinuity of the square norm as a convex function guarantees the estimate for the second term. Since
J^{*}
is the infimum over all cost function values in
{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
,
\bar{u}
is a global minimizer of
\hat{J}
in
{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
.
\Box

In a next step, we will comment on the differentiability of the solution operator
\mathcal{S}:{\mathscr{U}}\to{\mathscr{Y}}
and the reduced cost function
\hat{J}:{\mathscr{U}}\to\mathbb{R}
. Proofs of the Lemmas
5.1.4
and
5.1.6
can be found in
[
Tre17
]
or
[
HPUU09
, Section 1.6]
.

Lemma 5.1.4
.

The solution operator
\mathcal{S}
is twice continuously Fréchet differentiable in every
u\in L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}})
and every direction
h\in L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}})
. Let
y=\mathcal{S}(u)
and
y^{h}=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(u)h
. Then
y^{h}
is the solution to the linearized state equation

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle y^{h}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+a(y^{h}(t),\varphi)+{\langle\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(y(t))y^{h}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}&={\langle(\mathcal{B}h)(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V},\\
y^{h}(0)&=0.\end{aligned}

(5.1.13)

for all
\varphi\in V
and f.a.a.
t\in(0,T]
.

Remark 5.1.5
.

The nonlinearity in (
5.1.13
) is given as

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(y(t))y^{h}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}=3\int_{\Omega}y(t,{\bm{x}})^{2}y^{h}(t,{\bm{x}})\varphi({\bm{x}})~\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}.

\blacksquare

Lemma 5.1.6
.

The reduced cost function
\hat{J}
is twice continuously Fréchet differentiable in every
u\in L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}})
. Its
{\mathscr{U}}
-gradient
\nabla\hat{J}(u)\in{\mathscr{U}}
is given by

\displaystyle[\nabla\hat{J}(u)]_{i}(t)=\int_{\Omega}\chi_{i}({\bm{x}})p(t,{\bm{x}})~\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}+\sigma u_{i},\quad\text{for }i=1,...,{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}},~\text{f.a.a. }t\in(0,T).

Here,
p\in{\mathscr{Y}}
is the solution to the adjoint equation for all
\varphi\in V
.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} -\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle p(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+a(\varphi,p(t))+{\langle N^{\prime}(y(t))p(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}&=0,&&\text{f.a.a. }t\in(0,T),\\
p(T)&=y(T)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}}&&\text{in }H.\end{aligned}

(5.1.14)

Of course, there exist similar representations for the second derivatives of
\mathcal{S}
and
\hat{J}
, cf.
[
Tre17
]
or
[
HPUU09
, Section 1.6]
.

5.1.4
POD error estimation

To find a solution to (
5.1.11
), we want to employ a descent method that is based on first-order gradient information and incorporates the POD-reduced model from Section
5.1.2
. After such a model is built, the objective is to avoid any superfluous evaluations of the expensive FE model, while at the same time keeping track of how well the reduced model works in context of the optimization. In order to do this, we have the following a-posteriori error estimator from
[
KTV13
]
available to us.

Theorem 5.1.7
.

Let
\bar{u}\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
be a locally optimal control for (
5.1.11
) that satisfies the following coercivity condition for some
\kappa>0
.

\displaystyle\hat{J}^{\prime\prime}(\bar{u})(v,v)\geq\kappa\|v\|_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}\quad\text{for all }v\in{\mathscr{U}}.

(5.1.15)

Then there exists a radius
r>0
such that for all
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
with
\|\bar{u}-u\|\leq r
, it holds

\displaystyle{\|\bar{u}-u\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\leq\frac{2}{\kappa}\,{\|\zeta_{u}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}.

(5.1.16)

Here,
\zeta_{u}\in{\mathscr{U}}
is given by the perturbation function

\displaystyle[\zeta_{u}]_{i}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{r l}\max\big(0,-[\nabla\hat{J}(u)]_{i}(t)\big),&\text{if }[u]_{i}(t)=[u_{\mathsf{a}}]_{i}(t),\\
\min\big(0,-[\nabla\hat{J}(u)]_{i}(t)\big),&\text{if }[u]_{i}(t)=[u_{\mathsf{b}}]_{i}(t),\\
-[\nabla\hat{J}(u)]_{i}(t),&\text{otherwise},\\
\end{array}\right.

The main problem in Theorem
5.1.7
when compared to Theorem
4.2.14
for the linear-quadratic case lies in the coercivity constant
\kappa>0
and the unspecified radius
r>0
in which (
5.1.16
) holds. From a numerical reality, actually evaluating this constant is a quite expensive and complex task. We refer to
[
Tre17
]
, where this manner has been thoroughly investigated. In some cases, the concrete problem structure can be exploited and
\kappa
is analytically known. An example for such a case will come up in Section
5.3
where the reference point function - a quartic nonlinearity - represents a nonquadratic cost function whose coercivity constant can nevertheless be estimated in the linear-quadratic multiobjective case.

5.2
State-constrained model predictive control

The basic problem (
P
) has only bilateral constraints on the control variable
u
. In this section, we will examine the case when the state variable
y
is likewise constrained by pointwise lower and upper bounds. Further, we present a Model Predictive Control algorithm to handle long-time horizon and parameter updates. In the last part, we show how to combine the previous methodology with the POD method in a successful way, utilizing an a-posteriori error estimate.

5.2.1
Problem formulation

We introduce the space
{\mathscr{W}}=L^{2}(Q)
. Let
\mathcal{E}:L^{2}(0,T;H)\to\mathscr{W}
be a linear, bounded operator mapping to this space. For a given state
y\in L^{2}(0,T;H)
, we deal with pointwise constraints of the following type

y_{\mathsf{a}}(t,{\bm{x}})\leq(\mathcal{E}y)(t,{\bm{x}})\leq y_{\mathsf{b}}(t,{\bm{x}})\quad\text{a.e. in }Q,

(5.2.20)

where
y_{\mathsf{a}}
,
y_{\mathsf{b}}\in{\mathscr{W}}
are given lower and upper bounds with
y_{\mathsf{a}}\leq y_{\mathsf{b}}
a.e. in
Q
. To obtain regular Lagrange multipliers we utilize a virtual control approach
[
KR09
,
Mec19
,
MV18
]
. Let
\varepsilon>0
be chosen and
w\in{\mathscr{W}}
an additional (artificial) control. Then (
5.2.20
) is replaced by the mixed constraints

\displaystyle y_{\mathsf{a}}(t,{\bm{x}})\leq\mathcal{E}y(t,{\bm{x}})+\varepsilon w(t,{\bm{x}})\leq y_{\mathsf{b}}(t,{\bm{x}})\quad\text{ a.e in }Q.

We introduce the Hilbert space

\displaystyle\mathscr{X}=W(0,T)\times{\mathscr{U}}\times{\mathscr{W}}

endowed with the common product topology. The quadratic cost functional
J:\mathscr{X}\to\mathbb{R}
is given by

\displaystyle J(x)=\frac{\sigma_{1}}{2}\int_{0}^{T}{\|y(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\sigma_{2}}{2}\,{\|y(T)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{\sigma}{2}\,{\|u-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}+\frac{\sigma_{w}}{2}\,{\|w\|}_{\mathscr{W}}^{2}

for
x=(y,u,w)\in\mathscr{X}
. Furthermore,
\sigma_{1},\,\sigma_{2}\geq 0
, and
\sigma,\sigma_{w}>0
. The set of admissible solutions is given by

\displaystyle{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}^{\varepsilon}}=\big\{x=(y,u,w)\in{\mathscr{X}}\,\big|\,y=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u,~y_{\mathsf{a}}\leq\mathcal{E}y+\varepsilon w\leq y_{\mathsf{b}}\text{ and }u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}\big\}.

Then the optimal control problem reads

\min J(x)\quad\text{s.t.}\quad x\in{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}^{\varepsilon}}.

(
\mathbf{P}_{\varepsilon}
)

Problem (

P
ε

) can also be formulated as a pure control constrained problem. We set
\hat{y}_{\mathsf{a}}=y_{\mathsf{a}}-\mathcal{E}\hat{y}\in{\mathscr{W}}
and
\hat{y}_{\mathsf{b}}=y_{\mathsf{b}}-\mathcal{E}\hat{y}\in{\mathscr{W}}
. Then (
5.2.20
) can be formulated equivalently in the variables
u
and
w
as follows:

\displaystyle\hat{y}_{\mathsf{a}}(t,{\bm{x}})\leq(\mathcal{E}\mathcal{S}u)(t,{\bm{x}})+\varepsilon w(t,{\bm{x}})\leq\hat{y}_{\mathsf{b}}(t,{\bm{x}})\quad\text{ a.e. in }Q.

(5.2.21)

In light of this, let us define
{\mathscr{Z}}={\mathscr{U}}\times{\mathscr{W}}
and introduce the according bounded and linear mapping
\mathcal{T}:{\mathscr{Z}}\to{\mathscr{Z}}

z=(u,w)\mapsto\mathcal{T}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}u\\
\varepsilon w+\mathcal{E}\mathcal{S}u\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathcal{I}_{\mathscr{U}}&0\\
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{S}&\varepsilon\mathcal{I}_{\mathscr{W}}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}u\\
w\end{array}\right),

(5.2.22)

where
\mathcal{I}_{\mathscr{U}}:{\mathscr{U}}\to{\mathscr{U}}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathscr{W}}:{\mathscr{W}}\to{\mathscr{W}}
stand for the identity operators in
{\mathscr{U}}
and
{\mathscr{W}}
, respectively. Using this operator and
\hat{z}_{a}=(u_{a},\hat{y}_{\mathsf{a}})
,
\hat{z}_{b}=(u_{b},\hat{y}_{\mathsf{b}})\in{\mathscr{Z}}
, (
5.2.21
) can equivalently be formulated as

\displaystyle z=(u,w)\in{{\mathscr{Z}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}=\big\{z=(u,w)\in{\mathscr{Z}}\,\big|\,\hat{z}_{a}\leq\mathcal{T}(z)\leq\hat{z}_{b}\big\}.

where
{{\mathscr{Z}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is closed and convex; cf.
[
HKV09
]
. Notice that
\mathcal{T}
is invertible and
\mathcal{T}^{-1}
is explicitly given as

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{-1}(\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{w})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathcal{I}_{\mathscr{U}}&0\\
-\varepsilon^{-1}\mathcal{E}\mathcal{S}&\varepsilon^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{\mathscr{W}}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathfrak{u}\\
\mathfrak{w}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathfrak{u}\\
(\mathfrak{w}-\mathcal{E}\mathcal{S}\mathfrak{u})/\varepsilon\end{array}\right)

for all
\mathfrak{z}=(\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{w})\in{\mathscr{Z}}
. In fact, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{-1}(\mathcal{T}(u,w))=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathcal{I}_{\mathscr{U}}&0\\
-\varepsilon^{-1}\mathcal{E}\mathcal{S}&\varepsilon^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{\mathscr{W}}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}u\\
\varepsilon w+\mathcal{E}\mathcal{S}u\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}u\\
w\end{array}\right)

for all
z=(u,w)\in{\mathscr{Z}}
and viceversa. Furthermore, we find

\mathcal{T}^{-\star}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathcal{I}_{\mathscr{U}}&-\varepsilon^{-1}\mathcal{S}^{\star}\mathcal{E}^{\star}\\
0&\varepsilon^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{\mathscr{W}}\end{array}\right):{\mathscr{Z}}\to{\mathscr{Z}},

(5.2.23)

with
\mathcal{T}^{-\star}=(\mathcal{T}^{\star})^{-1}
. Let
{\hat{y}^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}={y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}-\hat{y}\in L^{2}(0,T;H)
and
{\hat{y}^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}={y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-\hat{y}(T)\in H
. With this we introduce the reduced cost functional
\hat{J}:{\mathscr{Z}}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle\hat{J}(z)=J(\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u,u,w)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{1}}{2}\int_{0}^{T}{\|(\mathcal{S}u)(t)-{\hat{y}^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\sigma_{2}}{2}\,{\|(\mathcal{S}u)(T)-{\hat{y}^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}\|}_{H}^{2}+\frac{\sigma}{2}\,{\|u-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\sigma_{w}}{2}\,{\|w\|}_{\mathscr{W}}^{2}

for all
z=(u,w)\in{\mathscr{Z}}
. Then (

P
ε

) is equivalent to the following reduced problem

\min\hat{J}(z)\quad\text{s.t.}\quad z\in{{\mathscr{Z}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

(
\mathbf{\hat{P}}_{\varepsilon}
)

Applying standard arguments
[
Lio71
]
one can prove that there exists a unique optimal solution
\bar{z}=(\bar{u},\bar{w})\in{{\mathscr{Z}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
to (

^
P

ε

), assuming that the admissible set
{{\mathscr{Z}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is non-empty. The uniqueness follows from the strict convexity properties of the reduced cost functional.

5.2.2
Primal-dual active set strategy

Optimality conditions for problem (

^
P

ε

) can be derived following the optimization approach presented in Section
4.1.3
.

Theorem 5.2.8
.

Let Assumptions
3.2.1
and
4.1.8
hold. Suppose that the feasible set
{{\mathscr{Z}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is nonempty and that
\bar{z}=(\bar{u},\bar{w})\in{{\mathscr{Z}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is the solution to (

^
P

ε

) with associated optimal state
\bar{y}=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}\bar{u}
. Then there exist unique Lagrange multipliers
\bar{p}\in{\mathscr{Y}}
and
\bar{\nu}\in{\mathscr{W}}
,
\bar{\mu}=(\bar{\mu}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq\mathsf{m}}\in{\mathscr{U}}
satisfying the dual equation

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle\bar{p}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\varphi,\bar{p}(t))+{\langle(\mathcal{E}^{\star}\bar{\nu})(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\,{\langle({y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}-\bar{y})(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}~\forall\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in }[0,T),

(5.2.24)

\displaystyle\bar{p}(T)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\big({y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-\bar{y}(T)\big)

and the optimality system

\sigma(\bar{u}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p}+\bar{\mu}=0\text{ in }{\mathscr{U}},\quad\sigma_{w}\bar{w}+\varepsilon\bar{\nu}=0\text{ in }{\mathscr{W}}.

(5.2.25)

Moreover,

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}

\displaystyle=\max\big\{0,\bar{\nu}+\eta(\mathcal{E}\bar{y}+\varepsilon\bar{w}-y_{\mathsf{b}})\big\}+\min\big\{0,\bar{\nu}+\eta(\mathcal{E}\bar{y}+\varepsilon\bar{w}-y_{\mathsf{a}})\big\},

(5.2.26a)

\displaystyle\bar{\mu}_{i}

\displaystyle=\max\big\{0,\bar{\mu}_{i}+\zeta_{i}(\bar{u}-u_{bi})\big\}+\min\big\{0,\bar{\mu}_{i}+\zeta_{i}(\bar{u}_{i}-u_{ai})\big\}

(5.2.26b)

for
i=1\ldots,\mathsf{m}
, and for arbitrarily chosen
\eta,\zeta_{i}>0
, where the max- and min-operations are interpreted componentwise in the pointwise everywhere sense.

Remark 5.2.9
.

1)

Note that (
5.2.26a
) is a nonlinear complementarity problem (NCP) function based reformulation of the complementarity system

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}_{a}

\displaystyle\geq 0,

\displaystyle y_{\mathsf{a}}-\mathcal{E}\bar{y}-\varepsilon\bar{w}

\displaystyle\leq 0,

\displaystyle{\langle\bar{\nu}_{\mathsf{a}},y_{\mathsf{a}}-\mathcal{E}\bar{y}-\varepsilon\bar{w}\rangle}_{\mathscr{W}}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}_{b}

\displaystyle\geq 0,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\bar{y}+\varepsilon\bar{w}-y_{\mathsf{b}}

\displaystyle\leq 0,

\displaystyle{\langle\bar{\nu}_{\mathsf{b}},\mathcal{E}\bar{y}+\varepsilon\bar{w}-y_{\mathsf{b}}\rangle}_{\mathscr{W}}

\displaystyle=0

with
\bar{\nu}=\bar{\nu}_{b}-\bar{\nu}_{a}\in{\mathscr{W}}
. Analogously, (
5.2.26b
) is a NCP function based reformulation of the complementarity system

\displaystyle\bar{\mu}_{a}

\displaystyle\geq 0,

\displaystyle u_{a}-\bar{u}

\displaystyle\leq 0,

\displaystyle{\langle\bar{\mu}_{a},u_{a}-\bar{u}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\bar{\mu}_{b}

\displaystyle\geq 0,

\displaystyle\bar{u}-u_{b}

\displaystyle\leq 0,

\displaystyle{\langle\bar{\mu}_{b},\bar{u}-u_{b}\rangle}_{\mathscr{U}}

\displaystyle=0

with
\bar{\mu}=\bar{\mu}_{b}-\bar{\mu}_{a}\in{\mathscr{U}}
.

2)

Analogously to Lemma
4.1.22
and Remark
4.1.23
we split the adjoint variable
p
into one part depending on the fixed desired states and into two other parts, which depend linearly on the control variable and on the multiplier
\nu
. Let
\hat{p}\in{\mathscr{Y}}
be the solution to (
4.1.11
). Further, we define the linear, bounded operators
\mathcal{A}_{1}:{\mathscr{U}}\to{\mathscr{Y}}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}:{\mathscr{W}}\to{\mathscr{Y}}
as follows: for given
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
the function
p=\mathcal{A}_{1}u
is the unique solution to (
4.1.10
) and for given
\nu\in{\mathscr{W}}
the function
p=\mathcal{A}_{2}\nu
uniquely solves

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle p(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\varphi,p(t))

\displaystyle=-{\langle(\mathcal{E}^{\star}\nu)(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle\text{for all }\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in }[0,T),

\displaystyle p(T)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{in }H.

In particular, the solution
\bar{p}
to (
5.2.24
) is given by
\bar{p}=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}_{1}\bar{u}+\mathcal{A}_{2}\bar{\nu}
.
\blacksquare

It follows from Theorem
5.2.8
that the first-order conditions for (

^
P

ε

) can be equivalently written as the nonsmooth nonlinear system

\displaystyle\sigma\big(\bar{u}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\big)-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bar{p}+\bar{\mu}=0

\displaystyle\text{in }{\mathscr{U}},

(5.2.27a)

\displaystyle\sigma_{w}\bar{w}+\varepsilon\bar{\nu}=0

\displaystyle\text{in }{\mathscr{W}},

(5.2.27b)

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}=\max\big\{0,\bar{\nu}+\eta(\mathcal{E}\bar{y}+\varepsilon\bar{w}-y_{\mathsf{b}})\big\}+\min\big\{0,\bar{\nu}+\eta(\mathcal{E}\bar{y}+\varepsilon\bar{w}-y_{\mathsf{a}})\big\}

(5.2.27c)

\displaystyle\bar{\mu}_{i}=\max\big\{0,\bar{\mu}_{i}+\zeta_{i}(\bar{u}_{i}-u_{bi})\big\}+\min\big\{0,\bar{\mu}_{i}+\zeta_{i}(\bar{u}_{i}-u_{ai})\big\}

\displaystyle\text{for }i=1,\ldots,\mathsf{m}

(5.2.27d)

with the unknowns
\bar{u}
,
\bar{w}
,
\bar{\nu}
and
\bar{\mu}
. As presented for example in
[
MV18
]
, one can apply the primal-dual active set strategy (PDASS) in order to solve (
5.2.27
), which is equivalent to a semismooth Newton method
[
HIK03
]
and therefore super-linearly convergent. We choose
\eta=\sigma_{w}/\varepsilon^{2}>0
and
\zeta_{i}=\sigma>0
for all
i=1,\ldots,\mathsf{m}
. We indicate with
z^{k}=(u^{k},w^{k})\in{\mathscr{Z}}
the current semi-smooth Newton iterate for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and we set
y^{0}=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u^{0}
,
p^{0}=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}_{1}u^{0}-\sigma_{w}\mathcal{A}_{2}w^{0}/\varepsilon
. Moreover, from (
5.2.27a
)-(
5.2.27b
) we have

\displaystyle y^{k}

\displaystyle=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u^{k},

\displaystyle\nu^{k}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sigma_{w}}{\varepsilon}\,w^{k},

\displaystyle p^{k}

\displaystyle=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}_{1}u^{k}+\mathcal{A}_{2}\nu^{k},

\displaystyle\mu^{k}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}p^{k}-\sigma\big(u^{k}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\big).

Defining the associated active sets

\displaystyle{\mathscr{A}_{ai}^{\mathscr{U}}}(z^{k})

\displaystyle=\big\{t\in[0,T]\,\big|\,\mu_{i}^{k}+\sigma(u_{i}^{k}-u_{a})<0\text{ a.e.}\big\},\quad i=1,\ldots,\mathsf{m},

(5.2.28a)

\displaystyle{\mathscr{A}_{bi}^{\mathscr{U}}}(z^{k})

\displaystyle=\big\{t\in[0,T]\,\big|\,\mu_{i}^{k}+\sigma(u_{i}^{k}-u_{b})>0\text{ a.e.}\big\},\quad i=1,\ldots,\mathsf{m},

\displaystyle{\mathscr{A}_{a}^{\mathscr{W}}}(z^{k})

\displaystyle=\bigg\{(t,{\bm{x}})\in Q\,\big|\,\nu^{k}+\frac{\sigma_{w}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\big(y^{k}+\varepsilon w^{k}-y_{\mathsf{a}}\big)<0\text{ a.e.}\bigg\},

\displaystyle{\mathscr{A}_{b}^{\mathscr{W}}}(z^{k})

\displaystyle=\bigg\{(t,{\bm{x}})\in Q\,\big|\,\nu^{k}+\frac{\sigma_{w}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\big(y^{k}+\varepsilon w^{k}-y_{\mathsf{b}}\big)>0\text{ a.e.}\bigg\}

and associated inactive sets

\displaystyle{\mathscr{I}_{i}^{\mathscr{U}}}(z^{k})

\displaystyle=[0,T]\setminus\big({\mathscr{A}_{ai}^{\mathscr{U}}}(z^{k})\cup{\mathscr{A}_{bi}^{\mathscr{U}}}(z^{k})\big)\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,\mathsf{m},

(5.2.28b)

\displaystyle{\mathscr{I}^{\mathscr{W}}}(z^{k})

\displaystyle=Q\setminus\big({\mathscr{A}_{a}^{\mathscr{W}}}(z^{k})\cup{\mathscr{A}_{b}^{\mathscr{W}}}(z^{k})\big),

it holds that the new state
y^{k+1}
and the new adjoint
p^{k+1}
are given by the two coupled problems

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle y^{k+1}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;y^{k+1}(t),\varphi)-\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}}\chi_{{\mathscr{I}_{i}^{\mathscr{U}}}(z^{k})}(t)\frac{1}{\sigma}\int_{\Gamma}b_{i}p^{k+1}(t)\mathrm{d}\bm{\tilde{s}}\,\int_{\Gamma}b_{i}\varphi\mathrm{d}{\bm{s}}

\displaystyle={\langle\mathcal{F}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}+\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}}\big(\chi_{{\mathscr{A}_{ai}^{\mathscr{U}}}(z^{k})}(t)u_{ai}(t)+\chi_{{\mathscr{A}_{bi}^{\mathscr{U}}}(z^{k})}(t)u_{bi}(t)+\chi_{{\mathscr{I}_{i}^{\mathscr{U}}}(z^{k})}(t){u_{i}^{\mathsf{n}}}\big)\int_{\Gamma}b_{i}\varphi\mathrm{d}{\bm{s}}

\displaystyle\hskip 256.0748pt\forall\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in }(0,T],

\displaystyle y^{k+1}(0)=y_{\circ}.

and

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle p^{k+1}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;\varphi,p^{k+1}(t))+\sigma_{1}\,{\langle y^{k+1}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+\frac{\sigma_{w}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\,\left\langle y^{k+1}(t)\big(\chi_{{\mathscr{A}_{a}^{\mathscr{W}}}(z^{k})}(t)+\chi_{{\mathscr{A}_{b}^{\mathscr{W}}}(z^{k})}(t)\big),\varphi\right\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sigma_{1}\,{\langle{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+\frac{\sigma_{w}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\,\left\langle y_{\mathsf{a}}(t)\chi_{{\mathscr{A}_{a}^{\mathscr{W}}}(z^{k})}(t)+y_{\mathsf{b}}(t)\chi_{{\mathscr{A}_{b}^{\mathscr{W}}}(z^{k})}(t),\varphi\right\rangle_{H},

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\forall\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in }[0,T),

\displaystyle\quad p^{k+1}(T)=\sigma_{2}\big({y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}-y^{k+1}(T)\big),

respectively, which can be expressed as

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathcal{A}_{11}^{k}&\mathcal{A}_{12}^{k}\\[4.30554pt]
\mathcal{A}_{21}^{k}&\mathcal{A}_{22}^{k}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}y^{k+1}\\[4.30554pt]
p^{k+1}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{Q}_{1}(z^{k};y_{\circ},u_{a},u_{b},{u^{\mathsf{n}}},b_{i},\sigma)\\[4.30554pt]
\mathcal{Q}_{2}(z^{k};y_{\mathsf{a}},y_{\mathsf{b}},{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}},{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}},\varepsilon,\sigma_{w},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\end{array}\right).

(5.2.29)

We have
\mathcal{A}_{11}^{k}=\mathcal{H}+\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{11}^{k}
and
\mathcal{A}_{22}^{k}=\mathcal{H}^{\star}+\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{22}^{k}
, where the
k
-independent operator
\mathcal{H}:W(0,T)\to L^{2}(0,T,V^{\prime})
is defined as

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{H}y,\varphi\rangle}_{L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime}),L^{2}(0,T;V)}=\int_{0}^{T}{\langle y_{t}(t),\varphi(t)\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}+a(t;y(t),\varphi(t))\,\mathrm{d}t

for
y\in W(0,T)
and
\varphi\in L^{2}(0,T;V)
. Furthermore, the new control variable
z^{k+1}=(u^{k+1},w^{k+1})
is given by the linear system

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}b_{i}p^{k+1}\mathrm{d}{\bm{s}}-\sigma(u_{i}^{k+1}-{u_{i}^{\mathsf{n}}})

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{in }{\mathscr{I}_{i}^{\mathscr{U}}}(z^{k}),

\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,\mathsf{m},

(5.2.30)

\displaystyle u_{i}^{k+1}

\displaystyle=u_{a}

\displaystyle\text{in }{\mathscr{A}_{ai}^{\mathscr{U}}}(z^{k}),

\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,\mathsf{m},

\displaystyle u_{i}^{k+1}

\displaystyle=u_{b}

\displaystyle\text{in }{\mathscr{A}_{bi}^{\mathscr{U}}}(z^{k}),

\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,\mathsf{m},

\displaystyle w^{k+1}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{in }{\mathscr{I}^{\mathscr{W}}}(z^{k}),

\displaystyle y^{k+1}+\varepsilon\,w^{k+1}

\displaystyle=y_{\mathsf{a}}

\displaystyle\text{in }{\mathscr{A}_{a}^{\mathscr{W}}}(z^{k}),

\displaystyle y^{k+1}+\varepsilon\,w^{k+1}

\displaystyle=y_{\mathsf{b}}

\displaystyle\text{in }{\mathscr{A}_{b}^{\mathscr{W}}}(z^{k}).

We resume the previous strategy in Algorithm
5.2.2
.

1:

Choose starting value
z^{0}=(u^{0},w^{0})\in{\mathscr{Z}}
; set
k=0
and
flag
=
false
;

Determine
y^{0}=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u^{0}
and

;

3:

Get
{\mathscr{A}_{ai}^{\mathscr{U}}}(z^{0})
,
{\mathscr{A}_{bi}^{\mathscr{U}}}(z^{0})
,
{\mathscr{I}_{i}^{\mathscr{U}}}(z^{0})
,
i=1,\ldots,\mathsf{m}
, and

,

from (

);

);

,

,

,

, and

,

if

\mathscr{A}_{a1}^{\mathscr{U}}(z^{k})=\mathscr{A}_{a1}^{\mathscr{U}}(z^{k-1})
