flag
=
true
;

Remark 5.2.10
.

For the numerical realization of system (
5.2.30
) one can use for example piecewise linear finite element in space and implicit Euler scheme in time. Unfortunately, the discretized linear system matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{2N_{x}N_{t}\times 2N_{x}N_{t}}
, where
N_{x}
corresponds to the number of finite element nodes and
N_{t}
to the number of time steps. It is clear that for a fine triangulation of the domain
\Omega
store the matrix
A
and solve the linear system may cause problem. The first problem can be avoided thanks to the sparse block structure of the matrix
A
that allows to use a matrix-free GMRES method (even if we still need to store the finite element blocks). The second problem can be solved applying POD. Moreover, it is still clear that the same problem may occur if the horizon
T
is large (or even infinite!). In this case, a Model Predictive Control (MPC) approach can be utilized; cf.
[
GP16
,
RM09
]
. To be precise, in this context the presence of a terminal target
{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}
may lead to ill-posed algorithm, depending on the target itself. Therefore, in the next section we set
\sigma_{2}=0
and we briefly introduce MPC, showing also how to combine it with POD in a successful way. Let us mention that there are several contributions in the literature, where MPC and POD are combined; cf., e.g.,
[
AV15
,
GU14
,
Mec19
,
MV19
]
.
\blacksquare

5.2.3
Model Predictive Control

The basic idea of MPC is to predict, stabilize and optimize a given dynamical system by reconstructing the optimal control
u(t)=\Phi(t,y(t))
in a feedback form. In order to do that, one has to solve repetitively many open-loop optimal control problems on smaller time horizons
N\Delta T<T
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, the open-loop control on the first time step is stored and used to compute the dynamical system trajectory, before solving the next open-loop problem on a shifted time horizon. More details on MPC and its properties can be found in
[
GP16
,
RM09
]
, for instance. Now, how can MPC be applied to (

P
ε

)? As first, for chosen
0\leq t_{n}<t_{n}^{N}\leq T
with
t_{n}^{N}=t_{n}+N\Delta T
and
y_{n}\in H
we consider the following dynamical system on the time horizon
[t_{n},t_{n}^{N}]
:

y_{t}(t)=\mathcal{D}(t;y(t),u(t))\in V^{\prime}\text{ a.e. in }(t_{n},t_{n}^{N}],\quad y(t_{n})=y_{n}\text{ in }H.

(5.2.31)

where the time-dependent mapping
\mathcal{D}(t;\cdot,\cdot):V\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to V^{\prime}
is defined as

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{D}(t;\phi,\mathfrak{u}),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

\displaystyle=-a(t;\phi,\varphi)+{\langle f(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}+\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{m}}\mathfrak{u}_{i}(t)\int_{\Gamma}b_{i}({\bm{s}})\varphi({\bm{s}})\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{s}}

for
\phi,\varphi\in V
,
\mathfrak{u}=(\mathfrak{u}_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
a.e. in
[0,T]
. Furthermore, we consider the inequality constraints

\displaystyle u_{ai}(t)

\displaystyle\leq u_{i}(t)\leq u_{bi}(t),

\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,\mathsf{m}\text{ a.e. in }[t_{n},t_{n}^{N}],

(5.2.32)

\displaystyle y_{\mathsf{a}}(t,{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle\leq y(t,{\bm{x}})+\varepsilon w(t,{\bm{x}})\leq y_{\mathsf{b}}(t,{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle\text{a.e. in }Q_{n}=(t_{n},t_{n}^{N})\times\Omega.

Next we define the function spaces related to
[t_{n},t_{n}^{N}]

\displaystyle{\mathscr{U}}_{n}=L^{2}(t_{n},t_{n}^{N};\mathbb{R}^{m}),\quad{\mathscr{W}}_{n}=L^{2}(t_{n},t_{n}^{N};H),\quad{\mathscr{X}}_{n}=W(t_{n},t_{n}^{N})\times{\mathscr{U}}_{n}\times{\mathscr{W}}_{n}.

Moreover, let the set of admissible solutions be given as

\displaystyle{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}^{\varepsilon,n}}=\big\{x=(y,u,w)\in{\mathscr{X}}\,\big|\,~x\text{ satisfies \eqref{Section5.2:WeakDynamicalSystem-n} and \eqref{Section5.2:Eq:MPC-10}}\big\}.

Now, the open-loop problem can be adapted by choosing the following cost:

\displaystyle J_{n}(x)=\frac{\sigma_{1}}{2}\int_{t_{n}}^{t_{n}^{N}}{\|y(t)-{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}(t)\|}_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\sigma}{2}\,{\|u-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\|}_{{\mathscr{U}}_{n}}^{2}+\frac{\sigma_{w}}{2}\,{\|w\|}_{{\mathscr{W}}_{n}}^{2}

for
x=(y,u,w)\in{\mathscr{X}}_{n}
. Note that we choose
\sigma_{2}=0
as briefly justified in Remark
5.2.10
. The MPC method is summarized in Algorithm
5.2.3
.

0:

Initial state
y_{\circ}
, time horizon
N\Delta t
and regularization parameter
\varepsilon>0
;

1:

Set
t_{0}=0
and
y_{0}(0)=y_{\circ}
;

2:

for

n=0,1,2,\dots

do

3:

Set
t_{n}^{N}=t_{n}+N\Delta t
;

4:

Compute the optimal solution
\bar{x}_{n}^{\varepsilon}=(\bar{y}_{n}^{\varepsilon},\bar{u}_{n}^{\varepsilon},\bar{w}_{n}^{\varepsilon})
to

\min J_{n}(x)\quad\text{subject to}\quad x\in{{\mathscr{X}}_{\mathsf{ad}}^{\varepsilon,n}};

(
{\mathbf{P}}_{n}^{\varepsilon}
)

5:

Define the MPC feedback law
\Phi^{N}(t;y(t))=\bar{u}_{n}^{\varepsilon}(t)
for
t\in(t_{n},t_{n}+\Delta t]
;

6:

Get the MPC state
y_{n+1}
solving

\displaystyle(y)_{t}(t)=\mathcal{D}(t;y(t),\Phi^{N}(t;y(t)))\in V^{\prime}\text{ a.e. in }(t_{n},t_{n}+\Delta t],\quad y(t_{n})=y_{n}\text{ in }H;

7:

Set
t_{n+1}=t_{n}+\Delta t
.

8:

end

for

If
\Phi^{N}
is computed by the MPC algorithm, then the state
\bar{y}^{N}
solves (
5.2.31
) for the closed-loop control
\bar{u}^{N}=\Phi^{N}(\cdot\,;y(\cdot))
with a given initial condition
y_{\circ}
. Another advantage of MPC is that we can update the data during the for-loop. For example, suppose that we have a good forecast for the parameters involved in the problem until a certain time
\tilde{t}\in(0,T)
. Then, we can incorporate a new forecast at that time and update the data for the parameters in the next open-loop solves. This updating strategy can not be done when we solve (

P
ε

) directly with PDASS: we can not ensure the convergence of PDASS while changing the data during the iteration. Moreover, as already mentioned, when the horizon
T
is sufficiently large, it is not convenient, or even impossible, to use the PDASS directly, because of large memory consumption and high computational time. With MPC, instead, the horizon length depends on
N
, which is independent from
T
, although the larger
N
is chosen the better the MPC algorithm converges to the optimal solution in
[0,T]
; cf.
[
GP16
]
.

5.2.4
MPC-POD and a-posteriori error estimator

As already mentioned, one can think to apply POD to (
5.2.30
) improving the computational time. This can be essentially done as shown in Section
4.2
and Section
4.3
, therefore we do not repeat the procedure. The purpose of this section is to give an idea on how MPC and POD can be successfully combined. As first, note that in Algorithm
5.2.3
we solve (

P
n

ε

) in
[t_{n},t^{N}_{n}]
, therefore we compute an open-loop optimal control that can be used as an indicator of the future closed-loop control, which will be computed as
n
increases. Therefore, we expect that the snapshots computed through the open-loop optimal control can be close enough to the optimal solution, especially when the dynamics involved are not so complicated. Then, we propose the following method to generate the POD-basis: we solve (

P
n

ε

) by using the FE Galerkin scheme for
n=0
. Then, we take the state
\bar{y}_{0}^{\varepsilon}
and the associated adjoint variable
\bar{p}^{\varepsilon}_{0}
to build a POD basis of rank
\ell
as shown in Section
4.2
and Section
4.3
. Now, (

P
n

ε

) is solved for all
n>0
applying its (from now on) fixed POD Galerkin approximation. As said, we can expect that the POD Galerkin approximation is good enough for some subsequent steps
n
, but clearly at a certain point we need to update the basis. The quality of the approximation can be estimated using an a-posteriori error estimator, which is based on an perturbation argument
[
DHPY95
]
and has been already utilized in
[
TV09
]
, as the one in Section
4.2
. If such a-posteriori error is too big, we solve the current problem (

P
n

ε

) by using the FE Galerkin scheme and recompute the POD basis using the obtained optimal FE state and associated adjoint. As done in
[
GGV15
]
, this estimate can be generalized for the mixed control-state constraints case. At first, suppose that Assumptions
3.2.1
and
4.1.8
hold. Recall that the linear, invertible operator
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
has been introduced in (
5.2.22
). In particular,
z=(u,w)
belongs to
{{\mathscr{Z}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
if
\mathfrak{z}=(\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{w})=\mathcal{T}(z)\in{\mathfrak{Z}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
holds with the closed, bounded and convex subset

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{Z}_{\mathsf{ad}}}=\big\{\mathfrak{z}=(\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{w})\in{\mathscr{Z}}\,\big|\,u_{a}\leq\mathfrak{u}\leq u_{b}\text{ in }{\mathscr{U}}\text{ and }\hat{y}_{\mathsf{a}}\leq\mathfrak{w}\leq\hat{y}_{\mathsf{b}}\text{ in }{\mathscr{W}}\big\}\subset{\mathscr{Z}}.

Note that – compared to the definition of the admissible set
{{\mathscr{Z}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
– the set
{\mathfrak{Z}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
does not depend on the solution operator
\mathcal{S}
and on the regularization parameter
\varepsilon
. Now, we consider instead of (

^
P

ε

) the following optimal control problem

\min\hat{J}\big(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\mathfrak{z}\big)\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\mathfrak{z}=(\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{w})\in{\mathfrak{Z}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

(
\mathbf{\hat{\mathtt{P}}}^{\varepsilon}
)

If
\bar{z}=(\bar{u},\bar{w})
solves (

^
P

ε

), then
\bar{\mathfrak{z}}=\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{z})
is the solution to (

^
P

ε

). Conversely, if
\bar{\mathfrak{z}}
solves (

^
P

ε

), then
\bar{z}=\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(\bar{\mathfrak{z}})
is the solution to (

^
P

ε

). According to
[
GGV15
]
we have the following result.

Theorem 5.2.11
.

Suppose that Assumptions
3.2.1
and
4.1.8
hold. Let
\bar{z}=(\bar{u},\bar{w})
be the optimal solution to (

^
P

ε

). Then,
\bar{\mathfrak{z}}=\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}(\bar{z})
is the solution to (

^
P

ε

). Moreover, suppose that a point
\mathfrak{z}^{\mathsf{ap}}=(\mathfrak{u}^{\mathsf{ap}},\mathfrak{w}^{\mathsf{ap}})\in{\mathfrak{Z}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is computed. We set
z^{\mathsf{ap}}=\mathcal{T}^{-1}_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{z}^{\mathsf{ap}})
, i.e.,
z^{\mathsf{ap}}=(u^{\mathsf{ap}},w^{\mathsf{ap}})
fulfils
u^{\mathsf{ap}}=\mathfrak{u}^{\mathsf{ap}}
and
w^{\mathsf{ap}}=\varepsilon^{-1}\,(\mathfrak{w}^{\mathsf{ap}}-\mathcal{E}\mathcal{S}\mathfrak{u}^{\mathsf{ap}})
. Then, there exists a perturbation
\zeta=(\zeta^{u},\zeta^{w})\in{\mathscr{Z}}
, which is independent of
\bar{z}
, so that

{\|\bar{z}-z^{\mathsf{ap}}\|}_{\mathscr{Z}}\leq\frac{1}{\sigma_{z}}\,{\|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}^{\star}\zeta\|}_{\mathscr{Z}}\quad\text{with }\sigma_{z}=\min\{\sigma,\sigma_{w}\}>0.

(5.2.33)

where
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}^{\star}
denotes the adjoint of the operator
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
has a bounded inverse, the first claim follows. The second one can be shown by adapting the proof of Proposition 1 in
[
GGV15
]
.
\Box

Remark 5.2.12
.

The perturbation
\zeta
can be computed as follows: let
\xi=(\xi^{u},\xi^{w})\in{\mathscr{Z}}
be given as
\xi=\mathcal{T}^{-\star}\nabla\hat{J}(\bar{z}^{\mathsf{ap}})\in{\mathscr{Z}}
. Then,
\xi
solves the linear system
\mathcal{T}^{\star}\xi=\nabla\hat{J}(z^{\mathsf{ap}})
, i.e.,

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathcal{I}_{\mathscr{U}}&\mathcal{S}^{\star}\mathcal{E}^{\star}\\
0&\varepsilon\mathcal{I}_{\mathscr{W}}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\xi^{u}\\
\xi^{w}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sigma(u^{\mathsf{ap}}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}})-\mathcal{B}p^{\mathsf{ap}}\\
\sigma_{w}w^{\mathsf{ap}}\end{array}\right)

(5.2.34a)

where
p^{\mathsf{ap}}=\hat{p}+\mathcal{A}_{1}u^{\mathsf{ap}}
. Note that the first-order optimality conditions for problem (

^
P

ε

) can be written as

\displaystyle{\langle\xi+\zeta,\mathfrak{z}-\mathfrak{z}^{\mathsf{ap}}\rangle}_{\mathscr{Z}}\geq 0\quad\text{for all }\mathfrak{z}\in{\mathfrak{Z}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

Hence, we find

\zeta_{i}^{u}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\min\{0,\xi_{i}^{u}(t)\}&\text{for }t\in{\mathscr{A}_{ai}^{\mathscr{U}}}(z^{\mathsf{ap}}),\\[2.84526pt]
-\max\{0,\xi_{i}^{u}(t)\}&\text{for }t\in{\mathscr{A}_{bi}^{\mathscr{U}}}(z^{\mathsf{ap}}),\\[2.84526pt]
-\xi^{u}_{i}(t)&\text{for }t\in{\mathscr{I}_{i}^{\mathscr{U}}}(z^{\mathsf{ap}})\end{array}\right.

(5.2.34b)

for
i=1,\ldots,m
and

\zeta^{w}(t,{\bm{x}})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\min\{0,\xi^{w}(t,{\bm{x}})\}&\text{for }(t,{\bm{x}})\in{\mathscr{A}_{a}^{\mathscr{W}}}(z^{\mathsf{ap}}),\\[2.84526pt]
-\max\{0,\xi^{w}(t,{\bm{x}})\}&\text{for }(t,{\bm{x}})\in{\mathscr{A}_{b}^{\mathscr{W}}}(z^{\mathsf{ap}}),\\[2.84526pt]
-\xi^{w}(t,{\bm{x}})&\text{for }(t,{\bm{x}})\in{\mathscr{I}^{\mathscr{W}}}(z^{\mathsf{ap}}).\end{array}\right.

(5.2.34c)

\blacksquare

Finally, the MPC-POD algorithm is resumed in Algorithm
5.2.4
.

0:

Initial state
y_{\circ}
, time horizon
N\Delta t
, regularization parameter
\varepsilon>0
, rank of POD basis
\ell
and tolerance
\tau
for the a-posteriori estimate;

1:

Set
t_{0}=0
,
y_{0}(0)=y_{\circ}
, and
flag
=
true
;

2:

for

n=0,1,2,\dots

do

3:

Set
t_{n}^{N}=t_{n}+N\Delta t
;

4:

if

flag
=
true

then

5:

Compute the optimal solution
\bar{x}_{n}^{\varepsilon}=(\bar{y}_{n}^{\varepsilon},\bar{u}_{n}^{\varepsilon},\bar{w}_{n}^{\varepsilon})
to (

P
n

ε

);

6:

Compute a POD basis
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell
using as snapshots
\bar{y}_{n}^{\varepsilon}
and
\bar{p}_{n}^{\varepsilon}
;

7:

Define the MPC feedback law
\Phi^{N}(t;y(t))=\bar{u}_{n}^{\varepsilon}(t)
for
t\in(t_{n},t_{n}+\Delta t]
;

8:

Set
flag
=
false
;

9:

else

10:

Compute the POD suboptimal solution
\bar{x}_{n}^{\varepsilon,\ell}=(\bar{y}_{n}^{\varepsilon,\ell},\bar{u}_{n}^{\varepsilon,\ell},\bar{w}_{n}^{\varepsilon,\ell})
to (

P
n

ε

);

11:

Define the MPC feedback law
\Phi^{N}(t;y(t))=\bar{u}_{n}^{\varepsilon,\ell}(t)
for
t\in(t_{n},t_{n}+\Delta t]
;

12:

Compute the a-posteriori error estimate
e:={\|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}^{\star}\zeta\|}_{\mathscr{Z}}
from (

);

then

Set

=

;

and get the MPC state
y_{n+1}
solving

\displaystyle(y)_{t}(t)=\mathcal{D}(t;y(t),\Phi^{N}(t;y(t)))\in V^{\prime}\text{ a.e. in }(t_{n},t_{n+1}],\quad y(t_{n})=y_{n}\text{ in }H;

18:

end

for

Example 5.2.13
.

For this example we want to solve the guiding model problem with slight modification on its parameters and few additional requirements. In this case, we do not consider distributed control, assuming that there exists only a control on the boundary region
\Gamma_{1}=\{0\}\times[0.75,1.0]
, which is also where we have the inflow for the convection. We assume a different outside temperature

\displaystyle y_{\mathsf{out}}=15+\left(\frac{1}{2}+\cos(\pi t)\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\sin(\pi t)}{2}\right),

see Figure
5.2.1
.

Figure 5.2.1
:
Outside temperature
y_{\mathsf{out}}
.

We define also a modified advection field

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\bm{v}}}(t,{\bm{x}})=(0.1+0.1t){\bm{v}}({\bm{x}}),

where
{\bm{v}}
is the advection field introduced in
1.3
. Furthermore, we choose
u_{\mathsf{a}}=0
,
u_{\mathsf{b}}=10^{7}
as control constraints and

\displaystyle y_{\mathsf{a}}(t,{\bm{x}})=\min\left(16+\frac{t}{4},18\right),\quad y_{\mathsf{b}}(t,{\bm{x}})=32

as state constraints. The lower bound imposes a progressive rise of the minimum temperature in the room, instead the upper bound fix the value for the maximum temperature. Lastly we treat an economic MPC problem
[
GP16
]
, i.e., we choose
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}=0
and
\sigma=\sigma_{w}=1.0
. Therefore, the optimization is focused on keeping the temperature
y
inside the bounds using less control energy as possible. Note that, since we do not have a target, the existence of a non-trivial solution for the optimal control problem is guaranteed only by the presence of the state constraints.

We focus on three related solution approaches: MPC using the full-order model (MPC-FE), MPC using the POD model without updating the basis functions (MPC-POD-NU) and Algorithm
5.2.4
(MPC-POD) with the tolerance
\tau=3.0
. We point out that there is no general recipe to choose the tolerance
\tau
. Heuristically, one should consider the expected range for the optimal controls, according also to the control constraints. In the MPC framework, the possible determination of the turnpike of the closed-loop trajectory will give access to this expected range; cf.
[
GP16
]
. This expected range can be possibly determined also by solving the open-loop optimal control problem for a sufficiently large horizon. Of course, the tolerance
\tau
can be adjusted during the MPC algorithm. In our simulations, the magnitude of the controls for the open-loop problems was always in between 50 and 150, leading to the decision of a fixed
\tau=3.0
, which indicates our will to have a relative error of approximately the
3\%
between the full-order and reduced-order controls. The MPC open-loop time horizon is set to
N=120
and
\Delta t=5/199
.

The computed trajectory with the three MPC algorithms are reported in Figure
5.2.2
.

(b)

(c)

MPC-POD

:
MPC trajectory up to
t_{199}=5
.

As one can see, the minimum temperature in the domain
\Omega
at each time step (in blue) is not always greater than the constraint
y_{\mathsf{a}}
. This is caused by the virtual control regularization. For this example, the parameter
\varepsilon
is fixed in fact to 0.001 and therefore allows small violations of the constraints. The behavior of the virtual control approach diminishing the parameter
\varepsilon
is studied in
[
KR09
,
Mec19
]
. The average temperature (in violet) is anyway maintained inside the constraints’ range. Furthermore, Figure
5.2.2
shows that the MPC-POD-NU method (i.e. without basis updates) is not able to reconstruct the MPC-FE trajectory and even keeping the temperature inside the relaxed virtual constraints. This is confirmed by Table
5.2.2
, where the relative errors between the MPC-FE trajectory and the MPC-FE feedback control are reported. These errors are respectively defined as

\displaystyle\text{Rel.Err.}y=\frac{\|y^{\text{MPC-FE}}-y\|_{\mathscr{W}}}{\|y^{\text{MPC-FE}}\|_{\mathscr{W}}},\quad\text{Rel.Err.}u=\frac{\|u^{\text{MPC-FE}}-u\|_{\mathscr{U}}}{\|u^{\text{MPC-FE}}\|_{\mathscr{U}}}.

are reported also the computational time speed-up and the number of POD basis functions (also after each update for MPC-POD).

As one can notice, the risk is that
\ell
increases at each update, so that the MPC-POD method becomes slower. Furthermore, as Algorithm
5.2.4
proceeds,
\ell
can potentially become greater than the number of degrees of freedom of the full-order model. To avoid this, a more elaborated basis update strategy can be developed
[
Mec19
]
.

The computed feedback controls are shown in Figure
5.2.3
together with the MPC-POD-NU and MPC-POD error approximation of the MPC-FE control. Clearly, the MPC-POD-NU method can not reconstruct the full-order feedback control without updating the POD basis, due to the fact that the involved dynamics change according to the time-dependent parameters. A change of these parameters after the training horizon used for the POD method will cause an important lack in approximation. This lack in approximation can even be present since the begin, making necessary an immediate correction, as in this case (
\ell=21
). The subsequent basis updates happening respectively at time steps
n=\left\{4,17,42\right\}
(
\ell=\left\{33,48,64\right\}
) show anyway that the update of the POD model is important also during the MPC algorithm.
