5.3
Multiobjective PDE-constrained optimization

In real applications, optimization problems are often described by introducing several objective functions conflicting with each other. This leads to
multiobjective
or
multicriterial
optimization problems; cf.
[
Ehr05
,
Mie98
,
Sta88
]
. One prominent example is given by an energy efficient heating, ventilation and air-conditioning (HVAC) operation of a building with conflicting objectives such as minimal energy consumption and maximal comfort; cf.
[
FHC06
,
KTX11
]
. Finding the optimal control that represents a good compromise is the main issue in these problems. For that reason the concept of Pareto optimal or efficient points is developed. In contrast to scalar-valued optimization problems, the computation of a set of Pareto optimal points is required. Consequently, many scalar-valued constrained optimization problems have to be solved.

In this section we follow
[
Ban17
,
BBV16
,
BBV17
]
and apply the reference point method
[
Wie80
,
Wie86
,
RBW+09
]
in order to transform a bicriterial optimal control problem into a sequence of scalar-valued optimal control problems and solve them using well-known optimal control techniques; see
[
Trö10
]
. Let us mention that preliminary results combining reduced-order modeling and multiobjective PDE-constrained optimization have recently been derived; cf.
[
ITV16
,
IUV17
,
POBD16
]
.

In this section we will utilize the following notation: If
x^{1},x^{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
are two vectors, we write
x^{1}\leq x^{2}
if
x^{1}_{i}\leq x^{2}_{i}
for
i=1,2
, and analogously for
x^{1}\geq x^{2}
. For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
we set
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\geq x}=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,|\,y\geq x\}
and
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq x}=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,|\,y\leq x\}
. For brevity, we define
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq}:=\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq 0}
and
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\geq}:=\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\geq 0}
.

5.3.1
Problem formulation

The Hilbert space
{\mathscr{U}}
is either
\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}}
or
L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}})
with an open subset
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}_{\circ}}
. Let Assumption
4.1.8
hold. Suppose that
{\mathscr{H}}
is a real Hilbert space and
\mathcal{C}:{\mathscr{Y}}\to{\mathscr{H}}
a linear, bounded operator. Then we consider the following bicriterial optimal control problem:

\min J(y,u)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}J_{1}(y,u)\\
J_{2}(y,u)\end{array}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\|\mathcal{C}y-{y^{\mathsf{d}}}\|}_{\mathscr{H}}^{2}\\[2.84526pt]
{\|u-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}\end{array}\right),

(5.3.35a)

subject to
(y,u)\in{\mathscr{X}}={\mathscr{Y}}\times{\mathscr{U}}
solves

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle y(t),\varphi\rangle}_{H}+a(t;y(t),\varphi)

\displaystyle={\langle(f+\mathcal{B}u)(t),\varphi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}

\displaystyle\text{for all }\varphi\in V\text{ a.e. in }({t_{\circ}},{t_{\mathsf{e}}}],

(5.3.35b)

\displaystyle{\langle y({t_{\circ}}),\varphi\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle={\langle y_{\circ},\varphi\rangle}_{H}

\displaystyle\text{for all }\varphi\in H,

and

u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}=\big\{\tilde{u}\in{\mathscr{U}}\,\big|\,u_{a}\leq\tilde{u}\leq u_{b}\text{ in }{\mathscr{U}}\big\},

(5.3.35c)

where we additionally assume
u_{a},u_{b}\in L^{\infty}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}})
in the case of
U=L^{2}(\mathscr{D};\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}})
.

Recall that the solution to (
5.3.35b
) can be expressed as
y=\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u
. We set
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{C}\mathcal{S}
, which implies that
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{L}({\mathscr{U}},{\mathscr{H}})
holds.

Then we define the reduced objective
\hat{J}:{\mathscr{U}}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
by

\displaystyle\hat{J}(u)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\hat{J}_{1}(u)\\
\hat{J}_{2}(u)\end{array}\right)=J(\hat{y}+\mathcal{S}u,u)\quad\text{for }u\in{\mathscr{U}}

and consider the reduced bicriterial optimal control problem

\min\hat{J}(u)\quad\text{s.t.}\quad u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

(
\mathbf{\widehat{BP}}
)

Problem (

^
BP

) involves the minimization of a vector-valued objective. This is done by using the concept of order relation and Pareto optimality; cf.
[
Ehr05
]
, for instance.

Definition 5.3.14
.

The point
\bar{u}\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is called Pareto optimal for (

^
BP

) provided there is no other control
u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}\setminus\{\bar{u}\}
with
\hat{J}_{i}(u)\leq\hat{J}_{i}(\bar{u})
,
i=1,2
, and
J_{j}(u)<\hat{J}_{j}(\bar{u})
for at least one index
j\in\{1,2\}
.

The goal of multiobjective optimization is to compute (a numerical approximation of) the
Pareto set

\displaystyle{\mathscr{P}_{\mathsf{s}}}=\big\{u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}\,\big|\,u\text{ is Pareto optimal}\big\}\subset{\mathscr{U}}

and the
Pareto front

\displaystyle{\mathscr{P}_{\mathsf{f}}}=\big\{\hat{J}(u)\,\big|\,u\in{\mathscr{P}_{\mathsf{s}}}\big\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}.

In the next section we will introduce the Euclidean reference point method as a tool to achieve this goal. For the theoretical investigation we need the following properties of the functions
\hat{J}_{1}
and
\hat{J}_{2}
, which are proved in
[
BBV17
]
.

Lemma 5.3.15
.

1)

Both
\hat{J}_{1}
and
\hat{J}_{2}
are bounded from below.

2)

Both
\hat{J}_{1}
and
\hat{J}_{2}
are twice continuously Fréchet-differentiable.

3)

\hat{J}_{1}
is convex and
\hat{J}_{2}
is strictly convex.

4)

\hat{J}_{1}
is even strictly convex provided
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{C}\mathcal{S}
is injective.

Remark 5.3.16
.

Since the functions
\hat{J}_{1}
and
\hat{J}_{2}
are bounded from below, convex and continuous, and
{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
is bounded, the ideal vector
{y^{\mathsf{id}}}=(y^{\mathsf{id}}_{1},y^{\mathsf{id}}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
given as

\displaystyle y^{\mathsf{id}}_{i}=\min\big\{\hat{J}_{i}(u)\,\big|\,u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}\big\}

is well-defined.
\blacksquare

5.3.2
The Euclidean reference point method

One popular methodology to solve multiobjective optimization problems is the so-called scalarization method, where the multiobjective problem is transformed into a series of scalar optimization problems. These can then be solved by using well-known techniques for scalar optimization. Here we follow
[
BBV17
,
POBD16
,
RBW+09
]
: Given a
reference point

\displaystyle z=(z_{1},z_{2})\in{\mathscr{P}_{\mathsf{f}}}+\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq}=\big\{z+x\,\big|\,z\in{\mathscr{P}_{\mathsf{f}}}\text{ and }x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq}\big\}

we introduce the (Euclidean)
distance function

F_{z}:{\mathscr{U}}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
by

\displaystyle F_{z}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\,{\|\hat{J}(u)-z\|}_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\,\big(\hat{J}_{1}(u)-z_{1}\big)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\,\big(\hat{J}_{2}(u)-z_{2}\big)^{2}\quad\text{for }u\in{\mathscr{U}}.

Lemma 5.3.17
.

If
z\leq{y^{\mathsf{id}}}
holds,
F_{z}
is strictly convex.

Proof.
The distance functions has the form

\displaystyle F_{z}(u)=G_{1}(\hat{J}_{1}(u))+G_{2}(\hat{J}_{2}(u))

with
G_{i}(\xi)=\tfrac{1}{2}(\xi-z_{i})^{2}
being strictly monotonically increasing on
[z_{i},\infty)
. Since
\hat{J}_{1}
is convex and
\hat{J}_{2}
strictly convex by Lemma
5.3.15
,
F_{z}
itself is strictly convex because
\hat{J}(u)\geq y^{\mathsf{id}}\geq z
for all
u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
.
\Box

To find a point that approximates
z
as good as possible we consider

\min F_{z}(u)\quad\text{s.t.}\quad u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

(
\mathbf{\widehat{BP}}_{z}
)

Notice that (

^
BP

z

) is a scalar-valued optimization problem which is convex provided
z\leq{y^{\mathsf{id}}}
holds true. The next result is proved in
[
Ban17
]
.

Proposition 5.3.18
.

Let
z\in{\mathscr{P}_{\mathsf{f}}}+\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq}
. Then (

^
BP

z

) has a unique solution
\bar{u}_{z}=(\bar{u}_{zj})_{1\leq j\leq\mathsf{m}}
, which is Pareto optimal for (

^
BP

).

The solution
\bar{u}_{z}
can be characterized by the following optimality conditions; cf.
[
BBV17
]
.

Proposition 5.3.19
.

Let
z\in{\mathscr{P}_{\mathsf{f}}}+\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq}
and
\mathcal{G}
be injective. If the point
\bar{u}_{z}
is optimal for (

^
BP

z

), then the first-order necessary optimality condition

\big\langle(\hat{J}_{1}(\bar{u}_{z})-z_{1})\mathcal{G}^{\star}(\mathcal{G}\bar{u}_{z}+\hat{y}-{y^{\mathsf{d}}})+(\hat{J}_{2}(\bar{u}_{z})-z_{2})(\bar{u}_{z}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}),u-\bar{u}_{z}\big\rangle_{\mathscr{U}}\geq 0

(5.3.36)

holds for all
u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
. Moreover, if
z_{1}\leq\hat{J}_{1}(\bar{u}_{z})
and
z_{2}<\hat{J}_{2}(\bar{u}_{z})
we have that

\displaystyle{\langle F_{z}^{\prime\prime}(\bar{u}_{z})u^{\delta},u^{\delta}\rangle}_{{\mathscr{U}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{U}}}\geq\kappa_{z}\,{\|u^{\delta}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}\quad\forall u^{\delta}\in{\mathscr{U}},

where the hessian of
F_{z}^{\prime\prime}(u):{\mathscr{U}}\to{\mathscr{U}}^{\prime}
at a point
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
is given as

\displaystyle F_{z}^{\prime\prime}(u)u^{\delta}=\left\langle\big((\hat{J}_{1}(u)-z_{1})\mathcal{G}^{\star}\mathcal{G}+(\hat{J}_{2}(u)-z_{2})\mathcal{I}_{\mathscr{U}}\big)u^{\delta},\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathscr{U}}\quad\text{for }u^{\delta}\in{\mathscr{U}}

and
\kappa_{z}=\hat{J}_{2}(\bar{u}_{z})-z_{2}>0
. If even
z\leq{y^{\mathsf{id}}}
is valid, (
5.3.36
) is a sufficient optimality condition for (

^
BP

z

).

The idea is to solve (

^
BP

z

) for different appropriately chosen values of
z
to get a uniform approximation of the Pareto front. To be more precise, we want to construct a sequence
\{z^{(k)}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
of reference points along with optimal controls
\{u^{(k)}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
that solve (

^
BP

z

) with
z=z^{(k)}
as well as
\{\hat{J}^{(k)}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\hat{J}^{(k)}=\hat{J}(u^{(k)})
, such that a predefined quality criteria for the approximation of the Pareto front is fulfilled. That this is possible in theory is shown in the next proposition, which was proved in
[
Ban17
, Theorem 3.43, Theorem 3.47]
.

Proposition 5.3.20
.

1)

For any
u\in{\mathscr{P}_{\mathsf{s}}}
there is
z\in{\mathscr{P}_{\mathsf{f}}}+\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq}
such that
u
is the unique solution of (

^
BP

z

).

2)

Let
z^{(1)},z^{(2)}\in{\mathscr{P}_{\mathsf{f}}}+\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq}
be arbitrary and denote by
\bar{u}_{z^{(1)}},\bar{u}_{z^{(2)}}\in{\mathscr{P}_{\mathsf{s}}}
the unique solutions of the respective Euclidean reference point problems (

^
BP

z

). Then it holds

\left\|J(\bar{u}_{z^{(1)}})-J(\bar{u}_{z^{(2)}})\right\|\leq\left\|z^{(1)}-z^{(2)}\right\|.

(5.3.37)

The first statement of the previous proposition tells us that we can indeed compute every Pareto optimal control
u\in{\mathscr{P}_{\mathsf{s}}}
by solving a Euclidean reference point problem (

^
BP

z

). Moreover, the second statement implies that the distance between two reference points
z^{(1)},z^{(2)}
is an upper bound of the distance between the corresponding Pareto optimal points
J(\bar{u}_{z^{(1)}}),J(\bar{u}_{z^{(2)}})\in{\mathscr{P}_{\mathsf{f}}}
. This enables us to define an iterative sequence of reference points with a guaranteed upper bound on the maximal distance between two neighbouring Pareto optimal points. The exact procedure is the following: We first compute the minimizers of
\hat{J_{1}}
and
\hat{J}_{2}
, which are denoted by
\bar{u}^{(1)}
and
\bar{u}^{(\text{end})}
. Then the corresponding Pareto optimal points
\hat{J}^{(1)}:=\hat{J}(\bar{u}^{(1)})
and
\hat{J}^{(\text{end})}:=\hat{J}(\bar{u}^{(\text{end})})
mark the start and the end point of our iteration. Note that in our example computing the minimizer of
\hat{J}_{1}
numerically is quite challenging. Therefore, instead of the minimizer of
\hat{J}_{1}
, we compute the minimizer of the weighted-sum function
\hat{J}_{1}+\alpha\hat{J}_{2}
for a weight
0<\alpha\ll 1
, which is on the one hand a good approximation of the minimizer of
\hat{J}_{1}
and on the other hand regular enough to solve it numerically. Starting at the point
\hat{J}^{(1)}
, we generate the first reference point by the formula

z^{(1)}=\hat{J}^{(1)}+h^{||}\cdot\frac{(\alpha,-1)^{T}}{\left\|(\alpha,-1)^{\top}\right\|}+h^{\perp}\cdot\frac{(-1,-\alpha)^{\top}}{\left\|(-1,-\alpha)^{T}\right\|}.

(5.3.38)

Here, we make use of the fact that the Pareto front at the point
\hat{J}^{(1)}
is tangential to the vector
\left(\alpha,-1\right)^{T}
. Thus, the parameter
h^{||}>0
determines how far along the Pareto front the reference point will be chosen. By
h^{\perp}\geq 0
we can choose how far the new reference point should be located from the Pareto front. Analogously, one can show that after solving a Euclidean reference point problem (

^
BP

z

) to a reference point
z
, the vectors
\varphi^{\perp}=z-\hat{J}(\bar{u}^{z})
and
\varphi^{||}=(-\varphi^{\perp}_{2},\varphi^{\perp}_{1})^{\top}
are perpendicular and tangential to the Pareto front at the point
\hat{J}(\bar{u}^{z})
, respectively. This motivates the iterative formula for the reference points

z^{(i+1)}=z^{(i)}+h^{||}\frac{\varphi^{||}}{\left\|\varphi^{||}\right\|}+h^{\perp}\frac{\varphi^{\perp}}{\left\|\varphi^{\perp}\right\|}

(5.3.39)

for
i=1,\ldots
, see Figure
5.3.4
.

Figure 5.3.4
:

Graphical visualization of the iterative reference point generation (left) and a Pareto front which was computed by Algorithm
5.3.2
(right).

Using this formula it is guaranteed that

\left\|J^{(i+1)}-J^{(i)}\right\|\leq h^{||}

holds for all
i=1,\ldots
, which is why we can control the approximation quality by adapting the parameter
h^{||}
. We stop this iteration when the first component of the new reference point is larger than the first component of our end point
\hat{J}^{(\text{end})}
. The complete methodology is summarized in Algorithm
5.3.2
.

0:

Parameters
h^{||}>0
,
h^{\perp}\geq 0
, weighted-sum parameter
\alpha>0
.

1:

Minimize
\hat{J}_{1}+\alpha\hat{J}_{2}
and
\hat{J}_{2}
and save the solutions as
\bar{u}^{1}
and
\bar{u}^{(\text{end})}
. Set
\hat{J}^{(1)}:=\hat{J}(\bar{u}^{1})
and
\hat{J}^{(\text{end})}:=\hat{J}(\bar{u}^{(\text{end})})
.

2:

Compute
z^{(1)}
by (
5.3.38
).

3:

for

n=1,\ldots

do

4:

Solve (

^
BP

z

) with reference point
z^{(n)}
and save the solution as
\bar{u}^{(n+1)}
and
\hat{J}^{(n+1)}:=\hat{J}(\bar{u}^{(n+1)})
.

5:

Compute
z^{(n+1)}
by (
5.3.39
).

6:

if

z^{(n+1)}_{1}>\hat{J}^{(\text{end})}_{1}

then

7:

return

(\bar{u}^{(1)},\ldots,\bar{u}^{(n+1)},\bar{u}^{(\text{end})})
and
(\hat{J}^{(1)},\ldots,\hat{J}^{(n+1)},\hat{J}^{(\text{end})})

8:

end

if

9:

end

for

For more details we refer to
[
BBV17
, Section 6.1]
and
[
Ban17
, Section 3.4]
.

5.3.3
The POD approximation

In this section we want to show how the POD method can be successfully applied to the bicriterial optimal control problem (
5.3.35
). In particular, we will follow the outline of Section
4.2
and show a-priori convergence results and a-posteriori estimates. The analysis is only done for the continuous POD method, but can also be transferred to the POD method for the semidiscrete and the fully discrete approximation.

Suppose that we have computed a POD basis
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
of rank
\ell
. Then the reduced-order cost function is given as

\displaystyle\hat{J}^{\ell}(u)=J(\hat{y}^{\ell}+\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u,u)\quad\text{for }u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

Remark 5.3.21
.

From the definition it follows that
\hat{J}_{2}=\hat{J}_{2}^{\ell}
. However, to avoid misunderstandings we will still write
\hat{J}_{2}^{\ell}
if we want to refer to the second cost function in the POD case.
\blacksquare

The reduced-order bicriterial optimal control problem then reads

\min\hat{J}^{\ell}(u)\quad\text{s.t.}\quad u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}.

(
\mathbf{\widehat{BP}}^{\ell}
)

Its Pareto set and Paret front are denoted by
{\mathscr{P}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\ell}}
and
{\mathscr{P}_{\mathsf{f}}^{\ell}}
, respectively. For a given reference point
z\in{\mathscr{P}_{\mathsf{f}}^{\ell}}+\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq}
the Euclidean reference point problem (

^
BP

z

) is replaced by its reduced-order equivalent

\min F^{\ell}_{z}(u)\quad\text{s.t.}\quad u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}},

(
\mathbf{\widehat{BP}}^{\ell}_{z}
)

where the reduced-order distance function
F^{\ell}_{z}:{\mathscr{U}}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is given by

\displaystyle F^{\ell}_{z}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\,{\|\hat{J}^{\ell}(u)-z\|}_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\,\big(\hat{J}^{\ell}_{1}(u)-z_{1}\big)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\,\big(\hat{J}^{\ell}_{2}(u)-z_{2}\big)^{2}\quad\text{for }u\in{\mathscr{U}}.

Remark 5.3.22
.

The results of the Lemmas
5.3.15
and
5.3.17
, and the Propositions
5.3.18
and
5.3.20
still hold true for the reduced-order problem. Moreover, the first-order necessary optimality condition for the optimal solution
\bar{u}_{z}^{\ell}
of (

^
BP

ℓ

z

) is given by

\big\langle(\hat{J}_{1}^{\ell}(\bar{u}_{z}^{\ell})-z_{1})(\mathcal{G}^{\ell})^{\star}(\mathcal{G}^{\ell}\bar{u}_{z}^{\ell}+\hat{y}^{\ell}-{y^{\mathsf{d}}})+(\hat{J}_{2}^{\ell}(\bar{u}_{z}^{\ell})-z_{2})(\bar{u}_{z}^{\ell}-{u^{\mathsf{n}}}),u-\bar{u}_{z}^{\ell}\big\rangle_{\mathscr{U}}\geq 0

(5.3.40)

for all
u\in{{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{ad}}}
, where the operator
\mathcal{G}^{\ell}
is defined as
\mathcal{G}^{\ell}=\mathcal{C}\mathcal{S}^{\ell}
.
\blacksquare

POD a-priori analysis

In this section we deal with the a-priori analysis for the optimality system of the Euclidean reference point problem (

^
BP

z

), which is no linear-quadratic optimal control problem anymore due to the concatenation of the linear-quadratic cost functions with the Euclidean norm, for the continuous POD method. However, the idea and the procedure of our analysis strongly follows Section
4.2.3
. A-priori estimates for both the state and adjoint equation for the continuous POD method can be found in Section
3.3.2
and Section
4.2.2
. In particular, for a given reference point
z
we are interested in a-priori convergence results for the error between the solutions
\bar{u}
and
\bar{u}^{\ell}
to (

^
BP

z

) and (

^
BP

ℓ

z

), respectively. To be able to prove an a-priori convergence result for the optimal controls of (

^
BP

ℓ

z

) to the one of (

^
BP

z

), we first need to show the following result involving the first cost function
\hat{J}_{1}
and its gradient
\nabla\hat{J}_{1}
.

Lemma 5.3.23
.

Assume that Assumptions
4.1.1
,
4.1.8
and
4.2.2
for a
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
are satisfied. Then it holds

\left|\hat{J}_{1}(u)-\hat{J}_{1}^{\ell}(u)\right|^{2}\leq C\cdot\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{H}\big({\|\psi_{i}^{H}\|}_{V}^{2}+1\big)&&\text{for }X=H,~\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H},\\
&\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{H}\big\|\psi_{i}^{H}-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V}\psi_{i}^{H}\big\|_{V}^{2}&&\text{for }X=H,~\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{V},\\
&\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{V}{\|\psi_{i}^{V}-\mathcal{Q}_{H}^{\ell}\psi_{i}^{V}\|}_{V}^{2}&&\text{for }X=V,~\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H},\\
&\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{V}&&\text{for }X=V,~\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{V},\end{aligned}\right.

(5.3.41)

for a constant
C>0
independent of
\ell
and

\left\|\nabla\hat{J}_{1}(u)-\nabla\hat{J}_{1}^{\ell}(u)\right\|_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}\leq C\cdot\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{H}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{H}\big\|_{V}^{2},&&X=H,~\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H},\\
&\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{V}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{V}-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\psi_{i}^{V}\big\|_{V}^{2},&&X=V,~\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H},\end{aligned}\right.

(5.3.42)

for a constant
C>0
independent of
\ell
. In particular, it holds

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\left|\hat{J}_{1}(u)-\hat{J}_{1}^{\ell}(u)\right|=0,\quad\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\left\|\nabla\hat{J}_{1}(u)-\nabla\hat{J}_{1}^{\ell}(u)\right\|_{\mathscr{U}}=0.

(5.3.43)

Proof.

To prove (
5.3.41
) we note that it is possible to derive the following estimate for an arbitrary control
u\in{\mathscr{U}}
, (cf.
[
Ban17
, Proof of Theorem 5.31]
, where a similar estimate was shown for the case that
\hat{y}^{\ell}=\hat{y}
),

\displaystyle\left|\hat{J}_{1}(u)-\hat{J}_{1}^{\ell}(u)\right|

(5.3.44)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left|\big\|\mathcal{S}u+\hat{y}\big\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}+\big\|\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u+\hat{y}^{\ell}\big\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}\right|\big\|\mathcal{S}u+\hat{y}-(\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u+\hat{y}^{\ell})\big\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}

\displaystyle+\big\|\mathcal{S}u+\hat{y}-(\mathcal{S}^{\ell}u+\hat{y}^{\ell})\big\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}\big\|y_{d}\big\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}.

Now (
5.3.41
) follows from Theorem
3.3.14
and Corollary
3.3.9
. For (
5.3.42
) we can use the formula for the gradient to conclude

\displaystyle\big\|\nabla\hat{J}_{1}(u)-\nabla\hat{J}_{1}^{\ell}(u)\big\|_{\mathscr{U}}\leq\|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(0,T;V),{\mathscr{U}})}\big\|\mathcal{A}u+\hat{p}-(\mathcal{A}^{\ell}u+\hat{p}^{\ell})\big\|_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}.

(5.3.45)

Together with Theorem
4.2.8
this implies (
5.3.42
).

\Box

Now it is possible to show a-priori convergence with a convergence rate.

Theorem 5.3.24
.

Let
z\in\bigcap_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}({\mathscr{P}_{\mathsf{f}}^{\ell}}+\mathbb{R}_{\leq}^{2})\bigcap({\mathscr{P}_{\mathsf{f}}}+\mathbb{R}_{\leq}^{2})
be a reference point and denote by
\bar{u}
and
\bar{u}^{\ell}
the solutions to (

^
BP

z

) and (

^
BP

ℓ

z

), respectively. Assume that Assumptions
4.1.1
,
4.1.8
and
4.2.2
with
u=\bar{u}
hold. Let
\|\mathcal{Q_{H}^{\ell}}\|_{\mathcal{L}(V)}
be bounded independently of
\ell
. If it holds
\hat{J}_{2}(\bar{u})>z_{2}
, then

\big\|\hat{J}(\bar{u})-\hat{J}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell})\big\|^{2}+\big\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\big\|_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}\leq C\cdot\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{H}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{H}\big\|_{V}^{2},&&X=H,~\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{H},\\
&\sum_{i>\ell}\lambda_{i}^{V}\,\big\|\psi_{i}^{V}-\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\psi_{i}^{V}\big\|_{V}^{2},&&X=V,~\mathcal{P}^{\ell}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ell}_{H}.\end{aligned}\right.

(5.3.46)

In particular, it holds

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\big\|\hat{J}(\bar{u})-\hat{J}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell})\big\|+\big\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\big\|_{{\mathscr{U}}}=0.

(5.3.47)

Proof.

We follow the lines of the proof of
[
Ban17
, Theorem 5.41]
and get the estimate

\displaystyle\big\|\hat{J}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell})-\hat{J}(\bar{u})\big\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}^{2}+\left(\frac{\hat{J}_{1}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell})+\hat{J}_{1}(\bar{u})}{2}-z_{1}\right)\big\|\mathcal{S}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell}-\bar{u})\big\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H)}^{2}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{\hat{J}_{2}(\bar{u})+\hat{J}_{2}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell})}{2}-z_{2}\right)\big\|\bar{u}^{\ell}-\bar{u}\big\|_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\hat{J}_{1}(\bar{u})-z_{1}\right)\langle\nabla\hat{J}_{1}(\bar{u})-\nabla\hat{J}_{1}^{\ell}(\bar{u}),\bar{u}^{\ell}-\bar{u}\rangle_{U}+\left(\hat{J}_{1}(\bar{u})-\hat{J}_{1}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell})\right)\left(\hat{J}_{1}(\bar{u})-\hat{J}_{1}^{\ell}(\bar{u})\right).

Using
\hat{J}_{1}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell}),\hat{J}_{1}(\bar{u})\geq z_{1}
,
\hat{J}_{2}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell})\geq z_{2}
(which follow from
[
Ban17
, Corollary 3.41]
), the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and Young’s inequality we get

\displaystyle\big\|\hat{J}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell})-\hat{J}(\bar{u})\big\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\hat{J}_{2}(\bar{u})-z_{2}}{2}\big\|\bar{u}^{\ell}-\bar{u}\big\|_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C(\epsilon)\left(\hat{J}_{1}(\bar{u})-z_{1}\right)^{2}\big\|\nabla\hat{J}_{1}(\bar{u})-\nabla\hat{J}_{1}^{\ell}(\bar{u})\big\|_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}+\epsilon\,\big\|\bar{u}^{\ell}-\bar{u}\big\|_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\left(\hat{J}_{1}(\bar{u})-\hat{J}_{1}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell})\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\hat{J}_{1}(\bar{u})-\hat{J}_{1}^{\ell}(\bar{u})\right)^{2},

which yields

\displaystyle\big\|\hat{J}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell})-\hat{J}(\bar{u})\big\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}^{2}+\big\|\bar{u}^{\ell}-\bar{u}\big\|_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}\leq C\left(\big\|\nabla\hat{J}_{1}(\bar{u})-\nabla\hat{J}_{1}^{\ell}(\bar{u})\big\|_{\mathscr{U}}^{2}+\left(\hat{J}_{1}(\bar{u})-\hat{J}_{1}^{\ell}(\bar{u})\right)^{2}\right)

for a constant
C>0
independent of
\ell
. Now the claim follows from Lemma
5.3.23
.

Theorem
5.3.24
together with the continuity of
\hat{J}_{1}
and
\nabla\hat{J}_{1}
and a-priori convergence statements for the state and adjoint equation also yields a-priori convergence results for the cost functions and its gradients, cf.
[
Ban17
, Corollary 5.42]
.

Theorem 5.3.25
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
5.3.24
be satisfied. Then it holds

1)

\hat{J}_{1}(\bar{u}^{\ell})\to\hat{J}_{1}(\bar{u})
as
\ell\to\infty
.

2)

\nabla\hat{J}_{1}(\bar{u}^{\ell})\to\nabla\hat{J}_{1}(\bar{u})
and
\nabla\hat{J}_{1}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell})\to\nabla\hat{J}_{1}(\bar{u})
as
\ell\to\infty
.

3)

F_{z}(\bar{u}^{\ell})\to F_{z}(\bar{u})
and
F_{z}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell})\to F_{z}(\bar{u})
as
\ell\to\infty
.

4)

\nabla F_{z}(\bar{u}^{\ell})\to\nabla F_{z}(\bar{u})
and
\nabla F_{z}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell})\to\nabla F_{z}(\bar{u})
as
\ell\to\infty
.

POD a-posteriori analysis

The focus of this section is to discuss an a-posteriori estimate for the error
\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\|_{{\mathscr{U}}}
between the solution
\bar{u}
of (

^
BP

z

) and a suboptimal control
\bar{u}^{\ell}
, which is possibly – but not necessarily – the solution of (

^
BP

ℓ

z

). As in Section
4.2.4
we make use of a perturbation method (cf.
[
DHPY95
]
) and the fact that the cost functions
\hat{J}_{1}
and
\hat{J}_{2}
are still linear-quadratic. Thus, for details of the idea of the perturbation method we directly refer to Section
4.2.4
. Here, we only state the following theorem, which was proved in
[
Ban17
, Corollary 5.43 and Theorem 5.45]
.

Theorem 5.3.26
.

Suppose that Assumption
4.1.8
holds. Let
z\in{\mathscr{P}_{\mathsf{f}}}+\mathbb{R}_{\leq}^{2}
be a reference point and
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
be a POD basis of rank
\ell
. Define the function
\zeta^{\ell}\in{\mathscr{U}}
by

\displaystyle\zeta_{i}^{\ell}(t)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &-\min(0,(\nabla F_{z}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell}))_{i}(s))&&\text{a.e. in }\mathscr{A}^{\ell}_{\mathsf{a}i}=\big\{s\in\mathscr{D}\,|\bar{u}^{\ell}_{i}(s)=u_{\mathsf{a}i}(s)\big\},\\
&-\max(0,(\nabla F_{z}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell}))_{i}(s))&&\text{a.e. in }\mathscr{A}^{\ell}_{\mathsf{b}i}=\big\{s\in\mathscr{D}\,|\bar{u}^{\ell}_{i}(s)=u_{\mathsf{b}i}(s)\big\},\\
&-(\nabla F_{z}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell}))_{i}(t)&&\text{a.e. in }\mathscr{D}\setminus\big(\mathscr{A}^{\ell}_{\mathsf{a}i}\cup\mathscr{A}_{\mathsf{b}i}^{\ell}\big).\end{aligned}\right.

If
\hat{J}(\bar{u}^{\ell})\geq z
and
\hat{J}_{2}(\bar{u}^{\ell})>z_{2}
are satisfied, then the a-posteriori error estimates

{\|\bar{u}-\bar{u}^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\leq\left(\frac{\hat{J}_{2}(\bar{u}^{\ell})-z_{2}}{2}\right)^{-1}\,{\|\zeta^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{U}},\quad{\|\hat{J}(\bar{u})-\hat{J}(\bar{u}^{\ell})\|}_{\mathscr{U}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\hat{J}_{2}(\bar{u}^{\ell})-z_{2}}{2}\right)^{-1/2}\,{\|\zeta^{\ell}\|}_{\mathscr{U}}

(5.3.48)

hold. In particular, it also holds
\lim\limits_{\ell\to\infty}\|\zeta^{\ell}\|_{\mathscr{U}}=0
.

Remark 5.3.27
.

In contrast to the a-posteriori estimate in Theorem
4.2.14
, we also get an estimate for the error between the optimal and the suboptimal cost function values
\hat{J}(\bar{u})
and
\hat{J}(\bar{u}^{\ell})
. Also for the a-priori convergence statement in Theorem
5.3.24
we get a convergence rate for the error
\left\|\hat{J}(\bar{u})-\hat{J}^{\ell}(\bar{u}^{\ell})\right\|
– again in contrast to the a-priori convergence result in Theorem
4.2.12
. Together with the statement from Proposition
5.3.20
2), this is the main reason to use the Euclidean reference point method instead of the weighted-sum method (see e.g.
[
Ehr05
, Chapter 3]
) to solve the bicriterial optimal control problem (

^
BP

). By concatenating the cost functions with the Euclidean norm the Euclidean reference point problem gives us more geometrical information about the Pareto front, which can then be used to generate a uniform approximation of it, even in the case when POD is applied. This makes up for the fact that Euclidean reference point problems are not linear-quadratic anymore and thus harder to solve than the weighted-sum problems in general.
\blacksquare

Using the a-posteriori estimate from Theorem
5.3.26
we set up an algorithm using POD for model-order reduction and controlling the maximal error between the POD and the finite element solutions; see Algorithm
5.3.3
.

0:

Recursive parameters
h^{||}>0
,
h^{\perp}\geq 0
, weighted-sum parameter
\alpha>0
, initial length of POD basis
\ell_{0}
, number of incremented basis elements
\ell_{\text{incr}}
, maximal error in control space
\varepsilon_{\text{max}}
.

1:

Minimize
\hat{J}_{1}+\alpha\hat{J}_{2}
and
\hat{J}_{2}
and save the solutions as
\bar{u}^{(1),\ell}
and
\bar{u}^{(\text{end}),\ell}
. Set
\hat{J}^{(1),\ell}:=\hat{J}(\bar{u}^{(1),\ell})
and
\hat{J}^{(\text{end}),\ell}:=\hat{J}(\bar{u}^{(\text{end}),\ell})
.

2:

Compute a POD basis of length
\ell_{0}
using the snapshots
\mathcal{S}\bar{u}^{(1),\ell}+\hat{y}
and
\mathcal{A}\bar{u}^{(1),\ell}+\hat{p}
.

3:

Compute
z^{(1),\ell}
by (
5.3.38
).

4:

for

n=1,\ldots

do

5:

Set bool
\leftarrow
FALSE.

6:

while
bool is FALSE
do

7:

Solve (

^
BP

ℓ

z

) with reference point
z^{(n),\ell}
.

8:

Compute the a-posteriori estimate
\eta
from (
5.3.48
)

9:

if

\eta>\varepsilon_{\text{max}}

then

10:

Set size
\ell
of POD basis to
\ell\leftarrow\ell+\ell_{\text{incr}}
.

11:

else

12:

Save the solution to (

^
BP

ℓ

z

) as
\bar{u}^{(n+1),\ell}
and
\hat{J}^{(n+1),\ell}:=\hat{J}(\bar{u}^{(n+1),\ell})
.

13:

Set bool
\leftarrow
TRUE.

14:

end

if

15:

end

while

16:

Compute
z^{(n+1),\ell}
by (
5.3.39
).

17:

if

z^{(n+1),\ell}_{1}>\hat{J}^{(\text{end}),\ell}_{1}

then

18:

return

(\bar{u}^{(1),\ell},\ldots,\bar{u}^{(n+1),\ell},\bar{u}^{(\text{end}),\ell})
and
(\hat{J}^{(1),\ell},\ldots,\hat{J}^{(n+1),\ell},\hat{J}^{(\text{end}),\ell})
.

19:

end

if

20:

end

for

Of course, this simple POD basis extension strategy might not work for complex problems. More elaborate strategies to adapt the POD basis when necessary can be found in
[
Mak18
,
Spu19
,
BMV19
]
.

Chapter A
Appendix

A.1
Basics

Definition A.1.28

(Complex numbers)
.

1)

For a complex number
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
, let
\Re(\alpha)
and
\Im(\alpha)\in\mathbb{R}
denote its real and imaginary part, so we have
\alpha=\Re(\alpha)+\jmath\cdot\Im(\alpha)
where
\jmath
is the complex unity. The
complex conjugate
of
\alpha
is given by
\overline{\alpha}=\Re(\alpha)-\jmath\cdot\Im(\alpha)
.

2)

We write a vector
z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
as
z=(z_{i})_{i}
with
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\in\mathbb{C}
and a matrix
Z\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
as
Z=(Z_{ij})_{ij}
with
Z_{ij}\in\mathbb{C}
for
i=1,...,m
,
j=1,...,n
.
The vector
z
can then also be understood as a matrix
z\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times 1}
. For the matrix, we define its
transpose
as
Z^{\top}=(Z_{ij})_{ji}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times m}
and its
Hermitian
as
Z^{\mathsf{H}}=(\overline{Z}_{ij})_{ji}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times m}
.
.

3)

The canonical inner product in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
is given by
\langle y,z\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=y^{\top}\overline{z}
for
y,z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
. The induced norm is given by
\|z\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=\sqrt{\langle z,z\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}}
for
z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

4)

We write
|\cdot|_{2}
for the Euclidean norm, and
\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle_{2}
stands for the canonical inner product in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Recall that the Frobenius norm is given by

\displaystyle{\|{\bm{A}}\|}_{F}=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\big|A_{ij}\big|^{2}}\quad\text{for }{\bm{A}}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}.

Definition A.1.29

(Tensor notation)
.

Let
X
be a set,
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
and
b_{i}\in X
for
1\leq i\leq n
as well as
a_{ij}\in X
for
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq m
. Then we write
(b_{i})
for the vector
(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})\in X^{n}
and
((a_{ij}))
for the matrix
(a_{ij})_{1\leq i\leq n,\,1\leq j\leq m}\in X^{n\times m}
.

Lemma A.1.30

(Young’s inequality,
[
Alt92
]
)
.

For any real numbers
a,b,\varepsilon>0
and for all
p,q\in(1,\infty)
with
\tfrac{1}{p}+\tfrac{1}{q}=1
, it holds

\displaystyle ab\leq\frac{\varepsilon a^{p}}{p}+\frac{b^{q}}{q\varepsilon^{q/p}}

Definition A.1.31

(Gradient and Hessian)
.

1)

If
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a differentiable function with
m>1
, we write its
Jacobian matrix
as
\nabla f(x)=(\tfrac{\partial f_{i}}{\partial x_{j}}(x))_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. If
m=1
, we use this symbol for the
gradient

\nabla f(x)=(\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial x_{1}}(x),\ldots,\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial x_{n}}(x))^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

2)

If
f
is twice diffentiable with
m=1
, we denote the
Hessian matrix
by

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}f(x)=\nabla_{xx}f(x)=\big(\tfrac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}}(x)\big)_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\qquad\text{for }x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

Recall that if
f
is twice continuously differentiable, the Schwarz theorem states that
(\nabla^{2}f(x))^{\top}=\nabla^{2}f(x)
holds for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Proposition A.1.32

(Gronwall inequality,
[
Eva08
, p. 708]
)
.

Let
\eta:[{t_{\circ}},{t_{\mathsf{e}}}]\to\mathbb{R}
be a nonnegative, differentiable function satisfying

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime}(t)\leq\varphi(t)\eta(t)+\chi(t)\quad\text{a.e. in }({t_{\circ}},{t_{\mathsf{e}}}],

where
\varphi
and
\chi
are real-valued, nonnegative, integrable functions on
[{t_{\circ}},{t_{\mathsf{e}}}]
. Then,

\displaystyle\eta(t)\leq\exp\bigg(\int_{t_{\circ}}^{t}\varphi(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\bigg)\bigg(\eta({t_{\circ}})+\int_{t_{\circ}}^{t}\chi(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\bigg)

In particular, if
\chi=0
almost everywhere in
[0,T]
and
\eta(0)=0
hold true, we have
\eta=0
almost everywhere in
[0,T]
.

Setting
\tilde{\eta}(t)=\eta({t_{\circ}}+{t_{\mathsf{e}}}-t)
for
t\in[0,T]
we easliy derive the following result from Proposition
A.1.32
.

Corollary A.1.33
.

Let
\eta:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
be a nonnegative, differentiable function satisfying

\displaystyle-\eta^{\prime}(t)\leq\varphi(t)\eta(t)+\chi(t)\quad\text{a.e. in }(0,T],

where
\varphi
and
\chi
are real-valued, nonnegative, integrable functions on
[{t_{\circ}},{t_{\mathsf{e}}}]
. Then,

\displaystyle\eta(t)\leq\exp\bigg(\int_{t_{\circ}}^{t}\varphi(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\bigg)\bigg(\eta({t_{\mathsf{e}}})+\int_{t_{\circ}}^{t}\chi(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\bigg)

In particular, if
\chi=0
almost everywhere in
[0,T]
and
\eta({t_{\mathsf{e}}})=0
hold true, we have
\eta=0
almost everywhere in
[0,T]
.

Recall the following wel-known inequality.

Lemma A.1.34

(Bernoulli Inequality)
.

For
x\geq-1
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle(1+x)^{n}\geq 1+nx

(A.1.1)

A.2
Linear operators in Banach spaces

Definition A.2.35

(Banach space)
.

A complex vector space
X
together with a map
\|\cdot\|_{X}:X\to\mathbb{R}
is called a
normed space
if

1)

\|\varphi\|_{X}\geq 0
for all
\varphi\in X
and
\|\varphi\|_{X}=0
if and only if
\varphi=0
.

2)

\|\alpha\varphi\|_{X}=|\alpha|\cdot\|\varphi\|_{X}
for all
\varphi\in X
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
.

3)

\|\varphi+\phi\|_{X}\leq\|\varphi\|_{X}+\|\phi\|_{X}
for all
\varphi,\phi\in X
.

The space
X
is called a
Banach space
if it is additionally complete, meaning that every Cauchy sequence converges in
X
.

Definition A.2.36

(Hilbert space)
.

A complex vector space
X
together with a map
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{X}:X\times X\to\mathbb{C}
is called an
inner product space
if

1)

\langle\varphi,\varphi\rangle_{X}\geq 0
for all
\varphi\in X
and
\langle\varphi,\varphi\rangle_{X}=0
if and only if
\varphi=0
.

2)

The map
\langle\cdot,\varphi\rangle_{X}:X\to\mathbb{C}
is linear for all
\varphi\in X
.

3)

\langle\varphi,\phi\rangle_{X}=\overline{\langle\phi,\varphi\rangle}_{X}
for all
\varphi,\phi\in X
.

The space
X
is then also a normed space with the induced norm
\|\varphi\|_{X}=\sqrt{\langle\varphi,\varphi\rangle_{X}}
for all
x\in X
.
X
is called a
Hilbert space
if it is additionally complete. A subset
\mathscr{S}\subset X
is called
orthogonal
if it holds
\langle\phi,\psi\rangle_{X}=0
for all
\phi,\psi\in\mathscr{S}
with
\phi\neq\psi
. It is called
orthonormal
if we additionally have
\|\phi\|_{X}=1
for all
\phi\in\mathscr{S}
. Lastly,
\mathscr{S}
is called an
orthonormal basis
of
X
if it is orthonormal and its linear hull is dense in
X
. The orthogonal complement of
\mathscr{S}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathscr{S}^{\bot}=\big\{\varphi\in X\,\big|\,{\langle\varphi,\phi\rangle}_{X}=0\text{ for all }\phi\in\mathscr{S}\big\}.

The space
X
is called
separable
if it has a countable orthonormal basis
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\subset X
. Here we have
\mathbb{I}=\{1,...,n\}
(if
X
has the finite dimension
n\in\mathbb{N}
) or
\mathbb{I}=\mathbb{N}
(if
X
has infinite dimension).

Example A.2.37
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
the spaces
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathbb{C}^{n}
endowed with the respective inner products

\displaystyle{\langle x,y\rangle}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

\displaystyle=x^{\top}y=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}y_{i}\quad\text{ for all }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

\displaystyle\langle x,y\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}

\displaystyle:=x^{\top}\bar{y}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\bar{y}_{i}\quad\text{ for all }x,y\in\mathbb{C}^{n}

are Hilbert spaces. In both cases the induced norm is just given by the Euclidean norm. We refer to these inner products as the canonical inner product in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, respectively.
\blacklozenge

If
X
is separable with orthonormal basis
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
, it can be shown
[
RS80
]
that

\displaystyle\varphi=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\langle\varphi,\psi_{i}\rangle_{X}\psi_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad{\|\varphi\|}_{X}^{2}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{I}}|\langle\varphi,\psi_{i}\rangle_{X}|^{2}\qquad\text{for all }\varphi\in X.

The following result is proven in
[
RS80
, p. 43]
.

Theorem A.2.38

(Riesz isomorphism)
.

Let
X
be a Hilbert space with inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{X}
. Then the mapping

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{X}:X\to X^{\prime},\quad{\langle\mathcal{J}_{X}x_{1},x_{2}\rangle}_{X^{\prime},X}={\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle}_{X}

(A.2.2)

is an isometric isomorphism called the
Riesz isomorphism
.

Theorem A.2.39

(Projection theorem)
.

Let
X
be a Hilbert space and
M\subset X
a nonempty and closed subspace. Then the space
X
can be written as the direct sum
X=M\oplus M^{\perp}
, i.e., for every
\varphi\in X
, there is a unique decomposition
\varphi=\varphi_{M}+\varphi_{\perp}
with
\varphi_{M}\in M
and
\varphi_{\perp}\in M^{\perp}
. Furthermore,
\varphi_{M}
is the unique solution to the approximation problem

\displaystyle\min\limits_{\phi\in M}~~\|\varphi-\phi\|_{X}.

(A.2.3)

Proof
.
The fact that
X=M\oplus M^{\perp}
is a standard result and is proven, e.g., in
[
RS80
, Theorem II.3]
. Let now
\varphi=\varphi_{M}+\varphi_{\perp}\in X
with
\varphi_{M}\in M
and
\varphi_{\perp}\in M^{\perp}
and
\phi\in M
be arbitrarily given. It follows that

\displaystyle\|\varphi-\phi\|_{X}^{2}=\|\varphi_{M}-\phi\|_{X}^{2}+\|\varphi_{\perp}\|_{X}^{2}\geq\|\varphi_{\perp}\|_{X}^{2}=\|\varphi-\varphi_{M}\|_{X}^{2}.

Therefore,
\varphi_{M}
solves (
A.2.3
); cf. Figure
A.2.1
.
\Box

M
\varphi
\varphi_{M}
\varphi_{\perp}

Figure A.2.1
:
Visualization of the Theorem
A.2.39
.

Lemma A.2.40

(Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,
[
RS80
]
)
.

Let
X
be a Hilbert space with inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{X}
. Then it holds

\displaystyle\big|{\langle\varphi,\phi\rangle}_{X}\big|\leq{\|\varphi\|}_{X}{\|\phi\|}_{X}\qquad\text{for all }\varphi,\phi\in X.

Lemma A.2.41
.

Let
X
be an inner product space with inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{X}
. Then it holds

\displaystyle{\langle\varphi-\phi,\phi\rangle}_{X}=-\frac{1}{2}\,{\|\varphi-\phi\|}_{X}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{X}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\,{\|\phi\|}_{X}^{2}\quad\text{for all }\varphi,\phi\in X

(A.2.4)

Proof
.
We have

\displaystyle{\langle\varphi-\phi,\phi\rangle}_{X}+\frac{1}{2}\,{\|\varphi-\phi\|}_{X}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\,{\|\phi\|}_{X}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,{\langle\varphi-\phi,\varphi+\phi\rangle}_{X}+\frac{1}{2}\,{\|\phi\|}_{X}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{X}^{2}

where we have utilized the fact that
\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle_{X}
is bilinear.
\Box

Definition A.2.42

(Linear operators in Banach spaces)
.

Let
X
,
Y
be two complex Banach spaces. The operator
\mathcal{T}:X\to X
is called a linear, bounded operator if these conditions are satisfied:

1)

\mathcal{T}(\alpha_{1}\varphi+\beta\psi)=\alpha\mathcal{T}\varphi+\beta\mathcal{T}\phi
for all
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}
and
\varphi,\,\phi\in X
.

2)

There exists a constant
c>0
such that
\|\mathcal{T}\varphi\|_{Y}\leq c\,\|\varphi\|_{X}
for all
\varphi\in X
.

The set of all linear, bounded operators from
X
to
Y
is denoted by
\mathscr{L}(X,Y)
which is a Banach space equipped with the operator norm
[
RS80
, pp. 69-70]

\displaystyle{\|\mathcal{T}\|}_{\mathscr{L}(X,Y)}=\sup_{\|\varphi\|_{X}=1}{\|\mathcal{T}\varphi\|}_{Y}\quad\text{for }\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{L}(X,Y).

If
X=Y
holds, we briefly write
\mathscr{L}(X)
instead of
\mathscr{L}(X,X)
. For
\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{L}(X,Y)
the subspace

\displaystyle\mathrm{ran}\,\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}(X)\subset Y

is called the range of
\mathcal{T}
. The subspace

\displaystyle\mathrm{ker}\,\mathcal{T}=\{\varphi\in X\,|\,\mathcal{T}\varphi=0\}\subset X

is said to be the kernel or null space of
\mathcal{T}
.

Definition A.2.43

(Dual operators in Banach spaces)
.

Let
X,Y
be two complex Banach spaces and
\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{L}(X,Y)
. The dual operator
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
of a linear operator
\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{L}(X,Y)
is defined as

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{T}^{\prime}f,\varphi\rangle}_{X^{\prime},X}={\langle f,\mathcal{T}\varphi\rangle}_{Y^{\prime},Y}\quad\text{for all }(\varphi,f)\in X\times Y^{\prime},

where, for instance,
\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle_{X^{\prime},X}
denotes the dual pairing of the space
X
with its dual space
X^{\prime}=\mathscr{L}(X,\mathbb{C})
.

Definition A.2.44

(Adjoint operators in Hilbert spaces)
.

Let
X
and
Y
denote two complex Hilbert spaces. For a given
\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{L}(X,Y)
the adjoint operator
\mathcal{T}^{\star}:Y\to X
is uniquely defined by

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{T}^{\star}\phi,\varphi\rangle}_{X}={\langle\phi,\mathcal{T}\varphi\rangle}_{Y}=\overline{\langle\mathcal{T}\varphi,\phi\rangle}_{Y}\quad\text{for all }(\varphi,\phi)\in X\times Y.

With the Riesz isomorphism, we can draw a connection between the dual operator from Definition
A.2.43
and the adjoint operator from Definition
A.2.44
. This result is proven e.g. in
[
Trö10
, p. 186]
.

Theorem A.2.45
.

Let
X
and
Y
be two complex Hilbert spaces and
\mathcal{J}_{X}:X\to X^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{J}_{Y}:Y\to Y^{\prime}
denote the Riesz isomorphisms from Theorem
A.2.38
. For an operator
\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{L}(X,Y)
, it then holds

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{\star}=\mathcal{J}_{X}^{-1}\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\mathcal{J}_{Y}.

Moreover,
(\mathcal{T}^{\star})^{\star}=\mathcal{T}
.

Definition A.2.46
.

Let
X,Y
be two complex Hilbert spaces and
\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{L}(X,Y)
.

1)

If
X=Y
, the operator
\mathcal{T}
is called
self-adjoint
if
\mathcal{T}^{\star}=\mathcal{T}
.

2)

If
\mathcal{T}
is self-adjoint, we call it
nonnegative
if
\langle\mathcal{T}\varphi,\varphi\rangle_{X}\geq 0
for all
\varphi\in X
.

3)

\mathcal{T}
is called
compact
if for every bounded sequence
\{\varphi_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset X
, the sequence
\{\mathcal{T}\varphi_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset Y
contains a convergent subsequence.

Remark A.2.47
.

Let
X
be a complex Hilbert space. If
\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{L}(X)
is self-adjoint it is easy to show that each eigenvalue
\lambda
of
\mathcal{T}
fulfils
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. Additionally, if
\mathcal{T}
is even nonnegative, it holds
\lambda\geq 0
for each eigenvalue
\lambda
of
\mathcal{T}
.
\blacksquare

A.3
Spectral theory of linear operators

Definition A.3.48

(Spectrum and resolvent)
.

Let
X
be a complex Hilbert space and
\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{L}(X)
.

1)

A complex number
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
belongs to the
resolvent set

\rho(\mathcal{T})
if
\lambda\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{T}
is a bijection with a bounded inverse. Here,
\mathcal{I}\in\mathscr{L}(X)
stands for the identity operator in
X
. We define the
spectrum
of
\mathcal{T}
as
\sigma(\mathcal{T})=\mathbb{C}\setminus\rho(\mathcal{T})
.

2)

For a spectral point
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, if
\lambda\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{T}
is not injective,
\lambda
is said that to belong to the
point spectrum
of
\mathcal{T}
. In this case, a vector
\varphi\in X\setminus\{0\}
with
\mathcal{T}\varphi=\lambda\varphi
is called an
eigenvector
of
\mathcal{T}
.

To summarize, the complex numbers are separated into
\mathbb{C}=\sigma(\mathcal{T})\cup\rho(\mathcal{T})
and part of the spectrum
\sigma(\mathcal{T})
is made up of the point spectrum.

The following two theorems are essential for the spectral analysis of compact operators and can be found in
[
RS80
, p. 203]
.

Theorem A.3.49

(Riesz-Schauder)
.

Let
X
be a complex Hilbert space and
\mathcal{T}:X\to X
a linear, compact operator. Then the spectrum
\sigma(\mathcal{T})
is a discrete set having no limit points except perhaps
0
. Furthermore, every nonzero
\lambda\in\sigma(\mathcal{T})
belongs to the point spectrum of
\mathcal{T}
and the space of eigenvectors to
\lambda
is finite dimensional.

Theorem A.3.50

(Hilbert-Schmidt)
.

Let
X
be a complex separable Hilbert space and
\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{L}(X)
a compact and selfadjoint operator. Then there is a sequence of real eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
and corresponding eigenvectors
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\subset X
that form a complete orthonormal basis of
X
. If
X
has infinite dimension, we further get
\lambda_{i}\to 0
as
i\to\infty
.

The following result comes from
[
Kat95
, III-§6.4]
.

Theorem A.3.51
.

Let
X
be a Banach space,
\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{L}(X)
and
\Sigma\subset\sigma(\mathcal{T})
a part of the spectrum such that there exists a closed curve
\Gamma\subset\rho(\mathcal{T})
with
\Sigma
on the inside and
\Sigma^{\prime}:=\sigma(\mathbb{C})\setminus\Sigma
on the outside. Then there exists a decomposition
X=M\oplus M^{\prime}
which satisfies

1)

\mathcal{T}(M)\subset M
and
\mathcal{T}(M^{\prime})\subset M^{\prime}
.

2)

The spectra of the restricted operators
\mathcal{T}|_{M}\in\mathscr{L}(M)
and
\mathcal{T}|_{M^{\prime}}\in\mathscr{L}(M^{\prime})
coincide with
\Sigma
respectively
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Corollary A.3.52
.

Let
X
be a Banach space,
\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{L}(X)
be compact and self-adjoint and
\Sigma:=\{\lambda\}
with
\lambda
being a nonzero eigenvalue of
\mathcal{T}
. Then in the decomposition
X=M\oplus M^{\prime}
from Theorem
A.3.51
,
M
is given by the space of eigenvectors to
\lambda
.

Proof.

First of all, the closed curve
\Gamma\subset\rho(\mathcal{T})
may be chosen as a circle around
\lambda
. Since
\lambda
is nonzero, Theorem
A.3.49
tells us that it will be the only spectral point inside
\Gamma
if the radius is chosen small enough. Therefore, Theorem
A.3.51
is applicable. Furthermore, it was shown in Section III-§6.5 of
[
Kat95
]
that the operator
D_{\lambda}:=\lambda\mathcal{I}_{M}-\mathcal{T}|_{M}
is quasi-nilpotent. Because
\mathcal{T}
is compact, Theorem
A.3.50
tells us that
M
is finite-dimensional, so
D_{\lambda}
is even nilpotent. However, the only self-adjoint and nilpotent operator is zero which yields
\lambda\mathcal{I}_{M}=\mathcal{T}|_{M}
. In other words, every nonzero vector in
M
is an eigenvector to
\lambda
. On the other hand, if
x\in X
is an arbitrary eigenvector of
\mathcal{T}
, it can be written as
x=x_{1}+x_{2}
with
x_{1}\in M
,
x_{2}\in M^{\prime}
. Since
M,M^{\prime}
are
\mathcal{T}
-invariant by Theorem
A.3.51
, it follows that
\mathcal{T}x_{i}=\lambda x_{i}
for
i=1,2
. Because the spectrum of
\mathcal{T}|_{M^{\prime}}
does not contain
\lambda
, we infer
x_{2}=0
, so
x=x_{1}\in M
.
\Box

Remark A.3.53
.

In
[
Kat95
, IV-§3.5]
it is stated that the previous claim also holds true for a finite system of eigenvalues, i.e., if
\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{L}(X)
is compact and self-adjoint and
\Sigma=\{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\}
is a set of
n
distinct nonzero eigenvalues of
\mathcal{T}
, then in the decomposition
X=M\oplus M^{\prime}
from Theorem
A.3.51
,
M
is given by the space of eigenvectors to
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}
.
\blacksquare

The following result is taken from
[
Kat95
, IV-§3.4]
.

Theorem A.3.54
.

Let
X
be a Banach space,
\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{L}(X)
and
\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}\subset\sigma(\mathcal{T})
be parts of the spectrum separated by a closed curve
\Gamma\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\sigma(\mathcal{T})
that encloses
\Sigma
. Let further
X=M(\mathcal{T})\oplus M^{\prime}(\mathcal{T})
be the decomposition from Theorem
A.3.51
. Then there exists a
\delta>0
depending on
\mathcal{T}
and
\Gamma
with the following properties:

1)

Any
\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{L}(X)
with
\|\mathcal{S}-\mathcal{T}\|_{\mathscr{L}(X)}<\delta
has its spectrum
\sigma(\mathcal{S})
likewise split into two parts
\Sigma(\mathcal{S})
,
\Sigma^{\prime}(\mathcal{S})
separated by
\Gamma
. It holds
\Gamma\subset\rho(\mathcal{S})
.

2)

In the associated decomposition
X=M(\mathcal{S})\oplus M^{\prime}(\mathcal{S})
from Theorem
A.3.51
,
M(\mathcal{S})
and
M^{\prime}(\mathcal{S})
are isomorphic to
M(\mathcal{T})
and
M^{\prime}(\mathcal{T})
, respectively.

3)

Let
P(\mathcal{T})
,
P(\mathcal{S})\in\mathscr{L}(X)
denote the projections from
X
to
M(\mathcal{T})
and
M(\mathcal{S})
, respectively. Then it holds
P(\mathcal{S})\to P(\mathcal{T})
in
\mathscr{L}(X)
as
\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{T}
in
\mathscr{L}(X)
.

A.4
Integration and differentiation in Banach spaces

Definition A.4.55
.

Let
X
be a Banach space and
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{p}}
. A function
f:\mathcal{D}\to X
is called
measurable
if there exists a sequence
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of step functions
f_{n}:\mathcal{D}\to X
such that
f_{n}\to f
almost everywhere.
For
p\in[1,\infty)
, the
p
-th
Bochner space
is defined as

\displaystyle L^{p}(\mathcal{D};X)=\big\{f:\mathcal{D}\to X~\big|~f\text{ is measurable and }\int_{\mathcal{D}}\|f({\bm{x}})\|_{X}^{p}~\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}<\infty\big\}

Endowed with the norm

\displaystyle\|f\|_{p}:=\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathcal{D};X)}:=\bigg(\int_{\mathcal{D}}\|f({\bm{x}})\|_{X}^{p}~\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\bigg)^{1/p}\quad\text{ for all }f\in L^{p}(\mathcal{D};X),

The space
L^{p}(\mathcal{D};X)
is a Banach space. If
X=\mathbb{R}
holds, we write briefly
L^{p}(\mathcal{D})
instead of
L^{p}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R})
. If
X
together with the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{X}
is even a Hilbert space,
L^{2}(\mathcal{D};X)
is also a Hilbert space
[
AE01
]
endowed with the inner product

\displaystyle{\langle f,g\rangle}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};X)}=\int_{\mathcal{D}}{\langle f({\bm{x}}),g({\bm{x}})\rangle}_{X}~\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\qquad\text{for all }f,g\in L^{2}(\mathcal{D};X).

Furthermore, the essential Bochner supremum is defined for a measurable funnction
f:\mathcal{D}\to X
as

\displaystyle{\|f\|}_{\infty}={\|f\|}_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{D};X)}:=\inf\big\{C\geq 0~\big|~{\|f({\bm{x}})\|}_{X}\leq C\text{ for almost all }{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{D}\big\}.

Finally, the Bochner space of essentially bounded fuctions consists of all measurable functions
f:\mathcal{D}\to X
with
\|f\|_{\infty}<\infty
.

Theorem A.4.56

(Hölder inequality,
[
AE01
]
)
.

Let
X
be a Banach space and
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{p}}
. Then it holds

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{D}}\|f({\bm{x}})\|_{X}\cdot\|g({\bm{x}})\|_{X}~\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}~\leq~\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};X)}\cdot\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};X)}\quad\text{for all }f,g\in L^{2}(\mathcal{D};X).

In the following definition, we use the symbol
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
. An element
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathfrak{p}}
is called a
multi-index
of order
|\alpha|=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{p}}\alpha_{i}
. For a function
\varphi:\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{R}
that is
|\alpha|
-times classically differentiable, we use the short notation

\displaystyle\partial^{\alpha}\varphi({\bm{x}})=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}\right)^{\alpha_{1}}\ldots\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{\mathfrak{p}}}\right)^{\alpha_{\mathfrak{p}}}\varphi({\bm{x}})\quad\text{for }x\in\mathcal{D}.

Definition A.4.57

(Sobolev space)
.

Let
X
be a Banach space,
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{p}}
open and
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{D};X)
. For a multi-index
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}
, a function
f_{\alpha}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{D};X)
is called the
weak
\alpha
-derivative
of
f
if

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{D}}f({\bm{x}})\cdot\partial^{\alpha}\varphi({\bm{x}})~\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}=(-1)^{|\alpha|}\int_{\mathcal{D}}f_{\alpha}({\bm{x}})\cdot\varphi({\bm{x}})~\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{D})

Here, the space
C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{D})
is the space of all infinitely differentiable functions
\varphi:\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{R}
such that the support
\text{supp }\varphi=\overline{\{{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{D}~|~\varphi({\bm{x}})\neq 0\}}
is compact in
\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{p}}
. If
f
has such a weak
\alpha
-derivative, we indicate this by writing
\partial^{\alpha}f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{D};X)
and identify
\partial^{\alpha}f
with
f_{\alpha}
. For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the
Sobolev space

\displaystyle H^{k}(\mathcal{D};X)=\big\{f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{D};X)~\big|~\partial^{\alpha}f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{D};X)\text{ for }|\alpha|\leq k\big\}.

If
X
is is a Hilbert space,
H^{1}(\mathcal{D};X)
is also a Hilbert space
[
DL00
,
Eva08
]
endowed with the inner product

\displaystyle{\langle f,g\rangle}_{H^{1}(\mathcal{D};X)}={\langle f,g\rangle}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};X)}+\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{p}}{\langle f_{x_{i}},g_{x_{i}}\rangle}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};X)}\quad\text{for }f,g\in H^{1}(\mathcal{D};X).

Here, we have used the notion of weak partial derivatives
f_{x_{i}}=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}f=\partial^{\alpha}f
with
\alpha_{i}=1
and
\alpha_{j}=0
for
j\neq i
.

Definition A.4.58

(Gelfand triple)
.

Let
V
and
H
be real, separable Hilbert spaces such that
V
lies dense in
H
with compact embedding. We identify
H
with its dual space
H^{\prime}
so that we have the Gelfand triple

\displaystyle V\hookrightarrow H\simeq H^{\prime}\hookrightarrow V^{\prime}

where the last embedding is understood as follows: For every element
h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
and
v\in V
, we also have
v\in H
by the first embedding, so we can define
\langle h^{\prime},v\rangle_{V^{\prime},V}=\langle h^{\prime},v\rangle_{H^{\prime},H}
.

Let now
T>0
and the Hilbert spaces
V
,
H
form a Gelfand triple. Then we define the space

W(0,T)=L^{2}(0,T;V)\cap H^{1}(0,T;V^{\prime})=\big\{\varphi\in L^{2}(0,T;V)\,\big|\,\varphi_{t}\in L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})\big\}

(A.4.5)

The following properties of
W(0T)
are proven e.g. in
[
DL00
, pp. 472-479]
and
[
Trö10
, pp. 146-148]
.

Lemma A.4.59
.

Let
V,H
form a Gelfand triple and
T>0
.

1)

W(0,T)
is a Hilbert space endowed with the inner product

\displaystyle{\langle\varphi,\phi\rangle}_{W(0,T)}=\int_{0}^{T}{\langle\varphi(t),\phi(t)\rangle}_{V}+{\langle\varphi_{t}(t),\phi_{t}(t)\rangle}_{V^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}t\quad\text{for }\varphi,\phi\in W(0,T)

and the induced norm
\|\varphi\|_{W(0,T)}=\langle\varphi,\varphi\rangle_{W(0,T)}^{1/2}
.

2)

The space
W(0,T)
is continuously embedded into the space
C([0,T];H)
, the space of all continuous mappings from
[0,T]
to
H
. Thus, there exists a constant
C_{W}>0
such that

{\|\varphi\|}_{C([0,T];H)}\leq C_{W}\,{\|\varphi\|}_{W(0,T)}\quad\text{for all }\varphi\in W(0,T)

(A.4.6)

In particular,
\varphi(t)
,
t\in[0,T]
, is meaningful in
H
for an element
\varphi\in W(0,T)
.

3)

The integration by parts formula reads for all
\varphi,\phi\in W(0,T)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}{\langle\varphi_{t}(t),\phi(t)\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}{\langle\phi_{t}(t),\varphi(t)\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}\,\mathrm{d}t

(A.4.7)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\langle\varphi(t),\phi(t)\rangle}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}t={\langle\varphi({t_{\mathsf{e}}}),\phi({t_{\mathsf{e}}})\rangle}_{H}-{\langle\varphi({t_{\circ}}),\phi({t_{\circ}})\rangle}_{H}

4)

We have the formula

\displaystyle{\langle\varphi_{t}(t),\phi\rangle}_{V^{\prime},V}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,{\langle\varphi(t),\phi\rangle}_{H}\quad\text{for }(\varphi,\phi)\in W(0,T)\times V,\text{ f.a.a. }t\in[0,T].

Definition A.4.60

(Continuous functions)
.

Let
X,Y
be Banach spaces and
\mathcal{O}\subset X
an open subset. Then we denote by

\displaystyle C(\mathcal{O};Y):=\{f:\mathcal{O}\subset X\to Y\mid f\text{ is continuous}\}

the set of all continuous functions from
\mathcal{O}
to
Y
.

Definition A.4.61

(Gâteaux and Fréchet derivatives)
.

Let
X,Y
be real Banach spaces,
\mathcal{O}\subset X
an open subset and
f:\mathcal{O}\to Y
a given mapping.

1)

For a point
x\in\mathcal{O}
and a given direction
x^{\delta}\in X
, the
directional derivative
of
x
towards
x^{\delta}
is defined by

\displaystyle Df(x,x^{\delta}):=\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\big(f(x+\varepsilon x^{\delta})-f(x)\big)

provided the limit exists in
Y
.

2)

If the directional derivative exists for every direction
x^{\delta}\in X
and we have
Df(x,\cdot)\in\mathscr{L}(X,Y)
, then
f
is said to be
Gâteaux differentiable
at
x
with
Gâteaux derivative

f^{\prime}(x)=Df(x,\cdot)
. If
f
is Gâteaux differentiable in every
x\in\mathcal{O}
, we say that
f
is Gâteaux differentiable (in
\mathcal{O}
).

3)

If
f
is Gâteaux differentiable in
x\in\mathcal{O}
and it holds

\displaystyle\frac{\|f(x+x^{\delta})-f(x)-f^{\prime}(x)x^{\delta}\|_{Y}}{\|x^{\delta}\|_{X}}\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad x^{\delta}\to 0\text{ in }X,

then
f
is said to be
Fréchet differentiable
in
x
. If
f
is Fréchet differentiable in every
x\in\mathcal{O}
, we call
f
Fréchet differentiable (in
\mathcal{O}
). In that case, if the mapping

\displaystyle f^{\prime}:\mathcal{O}\to L(X,Y),\quad x\mapsto f^{\prime}(x)

(A.4.8)

is continuous,
f
is called
continuously Fréchet differentiable
(in
\mathcal{O}
).

Definition A.4.62

(Second-order Fréchet derivatives)
.

Let
X,Y
be real Banach spaces,
\mathcal{O}\subset X
an open subset and
f:\mathcal{O}\to Y
Fréchet differentiable. Then we call
f

twice Fréchet differentiable
in
x\in\mathcal{O}
if the function
f^{\prime}
from (
A.4.8
) is Fréchet differentiable in
x
, i.e. if a mapping
f^{\prime\prime}(x)\in L(X,L(X,Y))
exists with

\displaystyle\frac{\|f^{\prime}(x+x^{\delta})-f^{\prime}(x)-f^{\prime\prime}(x)x^{\delta}\|_{L(X,Y)}}{\|x\|_{X}}\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad x^{\delta}\to 0\text{ in }X.

In that case we call
f^{\prime\prime}(x)
the
second Fréchet derivative
of
f
in
x
. If
f
is twice Fréchet differentiable in every
x\in\mathcal{O}
,
f
is called twice Fréchet differentiable (in
\mathcal{O}
). If the mapping

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}:\mathcal{O}\to L(X,L(X,Y)),\quad x\mapsto f^{\prime\prime}(x)

is continuous,
f
is said to be
twice continuously Fréchet differentiable
(in
\mathcal{O}
).

Remark A.4.63
.

For two real Banach spaces
X
and
Y
, a function
A:X\times X\to Y
is said to be bilinear if for every
x\in X
, the functions
A(x,\cdot)
and
A(\cdot,x)
are linear mappings from
X
to
Y
. It is said to be bounded if there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\displaystyle{\|A(x_{1},x_{2})\|}_{Y}\leq c\,{\|x_{1}\|}_{X}{\|x_{2}\|}_{X}\quad\text{for all }x_{1},x_{2}\in X

If we denote the space of bilinear and bounded functions with
\mathscr{L}_{2}(X,Y)
and endow it with the norm

\displaystyle{\|A\|}_{\mathscr{L}_{2}(X,Y)}=\sup\big\{\|A(x_{1},x_{2})\|_{Y}~\big|~{\|x_{1}\|}_{X}={\|x_{2}\|}_{X}=1\big\},

it can be shown (see, e.g.,
[
Ger06
]
) that it is a Banach space and isometrically isomorph to the space
\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X,Y))
. Therefore, we can identify the second Fréchet derivative with a bilinear, continuous function
f^{\prime\prime}(x)\in\mathscr{L}_{2}(X,Y)
. Schwarz’s theorem
[
Ger06
]
states that
f^{\prime\prime}(x)
is always symmetric, so we have
f^{\prime\prime}(x)(x^{\delta}_{1},x^{\delta}_{2})=f^{\prime\prime}(x)(x^{\delta}_{2},x^{\delta}_{1})
for all directions
x^{\delta}_{1},x^{\delta}_{2}\in X
.
\blacksquare

Definition A.4.64

(Gradient and Hessian)
.

Let
X
be a real Hilbert space,
\mathcal{O}\subset X
open and
f:\mathcal{O}\to\mathbb{R}
Fréchet differentiable in
x\in\mathcal{O}
. With the Riesz isomorphism
\mathcal{J}_{X}:X\to X^{\prime}
, the
Gradient
of
f
at
x
is defined as
\nabla f(x)=\mathcal{J}_{X}^{-1}f^{\prime}(x)\in X
and satisfies

\displaystyle{\langle\nabla f(x),x^{\delta}\rangle}_{X}=f^{\prime}(x)x^{\delta}\quad\text{for all }x^{\delta}\in X.

If
f
is twice differentiable in
x
, the
Hessian
of
f
in
x
is defined as a mapping

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}f(x):X\to X,\quad\nabla^{2}f(x)x^{\delta}=\mathcal{J}_{X}^{-1}f^{\prime\prime}(x)x^{\delta}.

If we understand
f^{\prime\prime}(x)
as a bilinear function as mentioned in Remark
A.4.63
, it holds by definition of the Riesz isomorphism

\displaystyle{\langle\nabla^{2}f(x)x^{\delta}_{1},x^{\delta}_{2}\rangle}_{X}=f^{\prime\prime}(x)(x^{\delta}_{1},x^{\delta}_{2})\qquad\text{for all }x^{\delta}_{1},x^{\delta}_{2}\in X.

Since the Riesz isomorphism is an isometry, it holds
\|\nabla f(x)\|_{X}=\|f^{\prime}(x)\|_{X^{\prime}}
and
\nabla^{2}f(x)\in\mathscr{L}(X)
with
\|\nabla^{2}f(x)\|_{\mathscr{L}(X)}=\|f^{\prime\prime}(x)\|_{\mathscr{L}_{2}(X)}
.

Index

\overline{\alpha}

item 1)

a.e., almost everywhere

item 2)

Bilinear form

parameter-dependent,
a({\bm{\mu}};\cdot\,,\cdot)

§3.7.1

a(t;\cdot\,,\cdot)

,

,
Remark 4.1.3

a(\cdot\,,\cdot)

item 1)
,
§3.6.1

Control shape functions

§1.2

Cost functional

desired states,
{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{1}}
,
{y^{\mathsf{d}}_{2}}

§1.2

expected control,
{u^{\mathsf{n}}}

§1.2

nominal control,
{u^{\mathsf{n}}}
